Introductory University Physics — Contents Appendix A

Appendix ASolutions to the Exercises

Here we collect the complete solutions to the exercises at the end of each chapter. Before reading a solution, be sure to work through the problem yourself, pencil in hand, at least once. If you get stuck along the way, go back to the hints at the end of the chapter and to the worked examples in the main text, and then read this appendix. From the end of each solution, you can return to that chapter.

Chapter 1 Description of Motion — Position, Velocity, Acceleration, and Vectors

Exercise 1.1: Determining whether the particle approximation applies

Solution

In each case, estimate the size $L_{\text{obj}}$ of the object itself and the characteristic length $L_{\text{motion}}$ of the motion, and decide by the ratio $L_{\text{obj}}/L_{\text{motion}}$ (the criterion of Section 1.1).

(a) The trajectory of a baseball thrown by a pitcher. The diameter of the ball is about $7\ \mathrm{cm}=0.07\ \mathrm m$, and the pitching distance (from the pitcher to the catcher) is about $18\ \mathrm m$. The ratio is $0.07/18\approx4\times10^{-3}$, which is far smaller than 1. Therefore, if we are interested only in the flight path of the ball (the shape of its trajectory), it may be treated as a particle (point mass). (However, in problems that focus on the rotation of the ball itself, such as the curving of a pitched ball caused by its spin, the particle approximation cannot be used.)

(b) A figure skater spinning in place on the ice. As we saw in Example 1.1(b), the radius of rotation (the length of an outstretched arm, $\sim0.6\ \mathrm m$) and the representative size of the body ($\sim1\ \mathrm m$) are of the same order of magnitude, so the ratio is of order 1. Moreover, the phenomenon itself, that the skater spins faster when the arms are pulled in, depends on the positional relationship among the parts of the body, so the skater cannot be treated as a particle. We must treat the parts of the body as a collection of many particles (a system of particles, Chapter 10). (Since the shape changes when the arms are pulled in, the body is not a rigid body either.)

(c) The orbital motion of the Moon as seen from the Earth. The radius of the Moon is about $1.7\times10^3\ \mathrm{km}$, and the mean distance from the Earth to the Moon (the orbital radius) is about $3.8\times10^5\ \mathrm{km}$. The ratio is $1.7\times10^3/3.8\times10^5\approx4.5\times10^{-3}$, which is sufficiently small compared with 1. Therefore, in considering the orbit of the Moon, the Moon may be treated as a particle (however, in problems concerned with the rotation of the Moon or its deformation by tidal forces, the particle approximation cannot be used).

To summarize: in (a) and (c), “the size of the object itself is negligible compared with the scale of the motion,” so the particle approximation is a good approximation; in (b), however, the ratio is of order 1 and, in addition, the internal structure of the object itself (the positions of the arms) bears on the essence of the problem, so the particle approximation cannot be used.

Exercise 1.2: Deriving constant-acceleration motion in vector form by integration

Solution

In components, the acceleration vector is $\bm a=[a_x,a_y]=[0,-g]$. By the definition (acceleration is the time derivative of velocity), $\dd v_x/\dd t=0$ and $\dd v_y/\dd t=-g$, so we integrate both sides with respect to $t$.

$x$ component: integrating $\dd v_x/\dd t=0$ gives $v_x(t)=C_1$ (a constant). From the initial condition $v_x(0)=v_0\cos\theta$, we get $C_1=v_0\cos\theta$. Hence $v_x(t)=v_0\cos\theta$ (the velocity in the $x$ direction does not change).

$y$ component: integrating $\dd v_y/\dd t=-g$ gives $v_y(t)=-gt+C_2$. From the initial condition $v_y(0)=v_0\sin\theta$, we get $C_2=v_0\sin\theta$. Hence $v_y(t)=v_0\sin\theta-gt$.

Therefore the velocity vector is

$$ \bm v(t)=[\,v_0\cos\theta,\ \ v_0\sin\theta-gt\,] $$

Furthermore, integrating $v_x=\dd x/\dd t,\ v_y=\dd y/\dd t$ once more under the initial conditions $x(0)=0,\ y(0)=0$, we obtain

$$ x(t)=\int_0^t v_0\cos\theta\,\dd t'=v_0\cos\theta\cdot t $$ $$ y(t)=\int_0^t(v_0\sin\theta-gt')\,\dd t'=v_0\sin\theta\cdot t-\frac12gt^2 $$

That is, the position vector is

$$ \bm r(t)=\left[\,v_0\cos\theta\cdot t,\ \ v_0\sin\theta\cdot t-\frac12gt^2\,\right] $$

This is nothing more than carrying out the “generalization to vectors” of Section 1.4 (applying Theorem 1.1 independently to each component), with the $x$ component as motion with constant acceleration $a_x=0$ (in fact, uniform motion) and the $y$ component as motion with constant acceleration $a_y=-g$; the result is exactly the standard formulas of projectile motion without air resistance (treated in earnest in Chapter 5).

Exercise 1.3: The $v$-$t$ graph and the $a$-$t$ graph of free fall

Solution

Taking vertically upward as positive, with $v_0=0,\ a=-g$ ($g=9.8\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$), the velocity is $v(t)=-gt$.

The $v$-$t$ graph. Since $v(t)=-gt$ is a linear function of $t$, the $v$-$t$ graph is a straight line sloping downward to the right through the origin $(0,0)$. Its slope is $-g=-9.8\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$ (constant), which expresses that the (downward) speed keeps increasing with time. The intercept (the value at $t=0$) is 0.

The $a$-$t$ graph. Since the acceleration is $a=-g$ (a constant), the $a$-$t$ graph is a horizontal straight line on which the value on the vertical axis is constant at $-9.8\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$ (parallel to the $t$ axis).

Applying the relation “the slope is the derivative” of Section 1.6, the slope of the $v$-$t$ graph ($-g$, constant) is exactly the acceleration $a=\dd v/\dd t$, which is consistent with the $a$-$t$ graph being a horizontal line at the constant value $-g$. Also, from the relation “the area is the integral,” we can confirm that the area under the $v$-$t$ graph from $0$ to $t$ (carrying a negative sign because it lies below the $t$ axis) agrees with the displacement over that interval, $y(t)-y(0)=-\frac12gt^2$ (Eq. (1.6) of Chapter 1).

Exercise 1.4: Velocity and acceleration in circular motion, and a proof that the speed is constant

Solution

(a) Differentiate $x(t)=R\cos\omega t,\ y(t)=R\sin\omega t$ with respect to $t$, each according to the differentiation formula for composite functions. Since $\dd(\cos\omega t)/\dd t=-\omega\sin\omega t$ and $\dd(\sin\omega t)/\dd t=\omega\cos\omega t$,

$$ \bm v(t)=[\,\dot x,\dot y\,]=[\,-R\omega\sin\omega t,\ \ R\omega\cos\omega t\,] $$

Differentiating once more,

$$ \bm a(t)=[\,\ddot x,\ddot y\,]=[\,-R\omega^2\cos\omega t,\ \ -R\omega^2\sin\omega t\,]=-\omega^2\,[\,R\cos\omega t,\ R\sin\omega t\,]=-\omega^2\bm r(t) $$

That is, the acceleration is always directed opposite to the position vector $\bm r(t)$ (toward the origin), and its magnitude is $\omega^2$ times that of the position vector.

(b) The square of the speed is

$$ \abs{\bm v(t)}^2=(-R\omega\sin\omega t)^2+(R\omega\cos\omega t)^2=R^2\omega^2\sin^2\omega t+R^2\omega^2\cos^2\omega t=R^2\omega^2(\sin^2\omega t+\cos^2\omega t) $$

By the fundamental trigonometric identity $\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1$ (used with $\theta=\omega t$), the quantity in parentheses equals 1, so

$$ \abs{\bm v(t)}^2=R^2\omega^2\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \abs{\bm v(t)}=R\omega\ (\text{constant, }\ t\text{-independent}) $$

Thus the speed $\abs{\bm v}=R\omega$ is constant, but as we saw in (a), the acceleration $\bm a=-\omega^2\bm r$ is not $\bm 0$, because the direction of the velocity vector changes continuously; this is a concrete example of the statement in Section 1.2 that “even if the speed is constant, the acceleration is not necessarily 0.”

(c) The inner product (dot product) is the sum of the products of the components. From the result of (a),

$$ \bm v\cdot\bm a=(-R\omega\sin\omega t)(-R\omega^2\cos\omega t)+(R\omega\cos\omega t)(-R\omega^2\sin\omega t)=R^2\omega^3\sin\omega t\cos\omega t-R^2\omega^3\sin\omega t\cos\omega t=0 $$ $$ \bm r\cdot\bm v=(R\cos\omega t)(-R\omega\sin\omega t)+(R\sin\omega t)(R\omega\cos\omega t)=-R^2\omega\sin\omega t\cos\omega t+R^2\omega\sin\omega t\cos\omega t=0 $$

Here $\bm v\cdot\bm a=0$ expresses that the acceleration is perpendicular to the velocity (a characteristic of a motion in which only the direction changes while the speed does not), and $\bm r\cdot\bm v=0$ expresses that the velocity is perpendicular to the radial direction, that is, along the tangent to the circle. (Another way to see this: $\dd\abs{\bm v}^2/\dd t=2\bm v\cdot\bm a$, and if $\abs{\bm v}$ is constant the left side is 0, so $\bm v\cdot\bm a=0$.)

Exercise 1.5: Testing orthogonality with the inner product

Solution

That the inner product is 0 is equivalent to the two vectors being orthogonal (Section 1.7). We compute the sum of the products of the components.

(a) $[1,2,3]\cdot[2,-1,0]=1\times2+2\times(-1)+3\times0=2-2+0=0$. Since the inner product is 0, the vectors are orthogonal.

(b) $[1,0,0]\cdot[0,1,1]=1\times0+0\times1+0\times1=0$. Since the inner product is 0, the vectors are orthogonal.

Since the inner product is 0 for both pairs, we have confirmed that both pairs are orthogonal.

Exercise 1.6: Computing a cross product and the right-hand rule

Solution

In the component formula for the cross product, $\bm a\times\bm b=[a_yb_z-a_zb_y,\ a_zb_x-a_xb_z,\ a_xb_y-a_yb_x]$, substitute $\bm a=[1,0,0]$ and $\bm b=[0,1,0]$ to obtain

$$ \bm a\times\bm b=[\,0\times0-0\times1,\ \ 0\times0-1\times0,\ \ 1\times1-0\times0\,]=[\,0,\ 0,\ 1\,] $$

The result $[0,0,1]$ is none other than the unit vector $\bm e_z$ in the direction of the $z$ axis (Section 1.2). That is, $\bm e_x\times\bm e_y=\bm e_z$. This agrees with the direction in which the thumb points when the fingers of the right hand are curled from the positive direction of the $x$ axis ($\bm a$) toward the positive direction of the $y$ axis ($\bm b$), that is, the positive direction of the $z$ axis, so the result is exactly what the right-hand rule (Figure 1.5) predicts.

Exercise 1.7: The dimension of the spring constant

Solution

From the equation of motion $F=ma$, the dimension of force is the product of the dimension of mass and the dimension of acceleration. The dimension of mass is $[\mathrm M]$ and that of acceleration is $[\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]$ (Sections 1.2 and 1.3), so

$$ [F]=[\mathrm M][\mathrm{L\,T^{-2}}]=[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}] $$

Hooke's law $F=kx$ must have equal dimensions on both sides by Law 1.1 (dimensional homogeneity), so $[F]=[k][x]$. Since $[x]=[\mathrm L]$,

$$ [k]=\frac{[F]}{[x]}=\frac{[\mathrm{M\,L\,T^{-2}}]}{[\mathrm L]}=[\mathrm{M\,T^{-2}}] $$

Written in SI base units, the dimension $[\mathrm{M\,T^{-2}}]$ becomes $\mathrm{kg/s^2}$, which is the unit of force, $\mathrm N=\mathrm{kg\,m/s^2}$, divided by the unit of length, $\mathrm m$; that is, it coincides with $\mathrm{N/m}$ ($\mathrm{kg/s^2}=(\mathrm{kg\,m/s^2})/\mathrm m=\mathrm{N/m}$). The reason the unit of the spring constant is usually written as $\mathrm{N/m}$ is precisely this result of dimensional analysis.

Chapter 2 Newton's Laws of Motion — Momentum and Angular Momentum

Exercise 2.1: Momentum and velocity of a particle under a constant force

Solution

We set up the impulse–momentum theorem of Chapter 2 ($\bm p(t_1)+\int_{t_1}^{t_2}\bm F\,\dd t=\bm p(t_2)$) as a one-dimensional problem along the $x$ axis, with $t_1=0,\ t_2=t$. Since the force $F$ is constant in time, the integral for the impulse reduces to a simple multiplication:

$$ p(t)=p(0)+\int_0^t F\,\dd\tau=p(0)+F\big[\tau\big]_0^t=p(0)+Ft $$

Since the particle was at rest at time $t=0$ ($p(0)=0$), we obtain

$$ p(t)=Ft $$

For the velocity, the definition of momentum $p=m\dot x$ gives $\dot x=p/m$, so the velocity is

$$ \dot x(t)=\frac{p(t)}{m}=\frac{Ft}{m} $$

(This agrees exactly with what is obtained by substituting $a=F/m$ (the equation of motion) into the relation $v=at$ that we learned in high school physics as “an object under a constant force undergoes motion with constant acceleration.” We see that the impulse–momentum theorem gives the same result directly from the relation impulse = change in momentum, without going through the quantity called acceleration.)

Exercise 2.2: A special case of elastic collision — exchange of velocities between equal masses

Solution

The velocity formulas for an elastic collision ($e=1$) in Chapter 2 were

$$ v_1'=\frac{(m_1-m_2)v_1+2m_2v_2}{m_1+m_2},\qquad v_2'=\frac{2m_1v_1+(m_2-m_1)v_2}{m_1+m_2} $$

Substitute $m_1=m_2=m$ into these. First, for $v_1'$,

$$ v_1'=\frac{(m-m)v_1+2mv_2}{m+m}=\frac{0\times v_1+2mv_2}{2m}=\frac{2mv_2}{2m}=v_2 $$

(Note that the first term $(m_1-m_2)v_1$ of the numerator vanishes, becoming exactly 0 because $m_1=m_2$.) Similarly, for $v_2'$,

$$ v_2'=\frac{2mv_1+(m-m)v_2}{m+m}=\frac{2mv_1+0\times v_2}{2m}=\frac{2mv_1}{2m}=v_1 $$

(This time the second term $(m_2-m_1)v_2$ of the numerator vanishes.) Therefore we have shown that

$$ v_1'=v_2,\qquad v_2'=v_1 $$

In other words, when two bodies of equal mass collide elastically, their velocities are exchanged completely. The well-known phenomenon with billiard balls (whose masses are nearly equal and whose collisions are nearly elastic) is a concrete example of exactly this result: when one ball is sent straight into a ball at rest, the incoming ball nearly stops, and the ball that had been at rest sets off rolling with the original velocity of the incoming ball.

Exercise 2.3: Condition for the torque to vanish

Solution

The magnitude of the cross product can be written, with $\theta$ ($0\leqq\theta\leqq\pi$) the angle between the two vectors $\bm r,\bm F$, as

$$ \abs{\bm N}=\abs{\bm r\times\bm F}=\abs{\bm r}\,\abs{\bm F}\sin\theta $$

Under the conditions $\bm r\neq\bm 0,\ \bm F\neq\bm 0$ (hence $\abs{\bm r}\neq0,\ \abs{\bm F}\neq0$), $\bm N=\bm 0$ ($\abs{\bm N}=0$) holds only when $\sin\theta=0$, that is, when $\theta=0$ ($\bm r$ and $\bm F$ point in the same direction) or $\theta=\pi$ ($\bm r$ and $\bm F$ point in exactly opposite directions). In other words, the torque is zero only when the direction of the force $\bm F$ is parallel to the position vector $\bm r$ (either the same direction or the opposite direction is allowed).

Put geometrically, the torque is zero when the “line of action” of the force $\bm F$ (the straight line drawn along the direction of the force vector) passes through the origin $O$. The more the line of action of the force is offset from the origin (that is, the more it fails to pass through the origin), the larger the torque (the effect of causing rotation) becomes, even for a force of the same magnitude—this agrees exactly with our experience, expressed by the principle of the lever, that “the farther from the fulcrum you push, the easier it is to turn.”

Exercise 2.4: Central force and conservation of angular momentum

Solution

That the force $\bm F$ is a central force, that is, that $\bm F$ is always parallel to the position vector $\bm r$, means that it can be written, using some real-valued function (scalar function) $f(t)$, as

$$ \bm F(t)=f(t)\,\bm r(t) $$

(When $f\gt0$, $\bm F$ points in the same direction as $\bm r$, that is, it is repulsive; when $f\lt0$, $\bm F$ points opposite to $\bm r$, that is, it is attractive. Universal gravitation and the electrostatic force have this form.) Then the torque is

$$ \bm N=\bm r\times\bm F=\bm r\times\big(f\bm r\big)=f\,(\bm r\times\bm r) $$

(the scalar $f$ can be taken out of the cross product). Now, the cross product of a vector $\bm r$ with itself is always the zero vector, by the derivation in Section 2.5 of this chapter (for exactly the same reason as when we showed that $\bm v\times\bm v=\bm 0$): $\bm r\times\bm r=\bm 0$. Therefore,

$$ \bm N=f\cdot\bm 0=\bm 0 $$

holds at every time $t$. On the other hand, by the rotational equation of motion (Law 2.5) $\bm N=\dd\bm L/\dd t$,

$$ \frac{\dd}{\dd t}\bm L=\bm N=\bm 0 $$

That is, since the time derivative of the angular momentum $\bm L$ is identically zero, $\bm L$ is constant in time (it is conserved). It is precisely for this reason that angular momentum is conserved for a body moving under a central force (as in the motion of the planets).

Exercise 2.5: The pairs of forces on a book lying on a desk

Solution

The following two forces act on the book.

  • Gravity $m\bm g$: the force with which the Earth pulls the book vertically downward ($m$ is the mass of the book, and $\bm g$ is the gravitational acceleration).
  • Normal force $\bm R$: the force with which the desk pushes the book vertically upward.

The book stays at rest because these two forces have equal magnitudes and opposite directions, so that the resultant force is zero (equilibrium of forces): $m\bm g+\bm R=\bm 0$. However, this is not a pair under the third law (the law of action and reaction)—the law of action and reaction is a statement about “forces between two different bodies,” whereas $m\bm g$ and $\bm R$ are two different kinds of forces that both act on the same body (the book).

To find the action–reaction pairs correctly, for each force acting on the book, we ask: “What reaction does the book exert back on the body on the other side?”

  • The partner of gravity $m\bm g$ is the force with which the book pulls the Earth vertically upward (by universal gravitation). This is a force acting on the Earth, not on the book.
  • The partner of the normal force $\bm R$ is the force with which the book pushes the desk vertically downward. This is a force acting on the desk, and again not on the book.

To summarize: the reason the book is at rest (equilibrium of forces) is that two different forces acting on the single body, the book (gravity and the normal force), cancel each other, and this is a different matter from the law of action and reaction (two pairs, one between the book and the Earth and one between the book and the desk, each holding separately). Not confusing the two is an important point for understanding Newtonian mechanics correctly.

Exercise 2.6: Collision with a wall and impulse

Solution

(1) Take the direction of motion before the collision (toward the wall) as the positive direction. The mass of the ball is $m=0.5\ \mathrm{kg}$. The momentum before the collision is $p_1=0.5\times8=4\ \mathrm{kg\,m/s}$, and after the rebound the direction is reversed, so $p_2=0.5\times(-6)=-3\ \mathrm{kg\,m/s}$. By the impulse–momentum theorem (Theorem 2.1), the impulse the ball received from the wall equals the change in momentum,

$$ I=p_2-p_1=-3-4=-7\ \mathrm{N\,s} $$

The magnitude of the impulse is $7\ \mathrm{N\,s}$, and its direction is the negative direction, that is, away from the wall (opposite to the direction of motion before the collision).

(2) Since the impulse equals “average force × contact time,” the magnitude of the average force is

$$ \bar F=\frac{\abs{I}}{\Delta t}=\frac{7\ \mathrm{N\,s}}{2.0\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm s}=3.5\times10^{3}\ \mathrm N $$

We see that a force as large as about $7\times10^{2}$ times the gravitational force $0.5\times9.8=4.9\ \mathrm N$ on the ball acts for a very short time.

(3) By Definition 2.6, since the velocity of the wall is $0$ both before and after the collision, the coefficient of restitution is

$$ e=-\frac{v_1'-0}{v_1-0}=-\frac{v_1'}{v_1}=-\frac{-6}{8}=0.75 $$

In general, if the velocity of the ball before the collision is $v_1=v\gt0$, the equation above gives the velocity after the collision as $v_1'=-ev$, and the change in the ball's momentum, that is, the impulse, is

$$ I=mv_1'-mv_1=-emv-mv=-(1+e)\,mv $$

whose magnitude is $(1+e)mv$. Substituting the values of this problem gives $(1+0.75)\times0.5\times8=7\ \mathrm{N\,s}$, in agreement with the result of (1). For $e=0$ (no rebound) the impulse is $mv$, and for $e=1$ (perfect rebound) it is $2mv$; the impulse is larger when the ball rebounds.

Exercise 2.7: The height of a bouncing ball

Solution

(1) Substituting $h=1.00\ \mathrm m,\ h'=0.64\ \mathrm m$ into $e=\sqrt{h'/h}$, which was derived in the Application in Section 2.6, we obtain

$$ e=\sqrt{\frac{0.64}{1.00}}=0.80 $$

This is the coefficient of restitution of the ball.

(2) Let $v$ be the speed just before the bounce. The speed just after the bounce is $ev$, and the height reached is proportional to the square of the speed, from $v'^2=2gh'$. Therefore the height is multiplied by $e^2$ at each bounce. The speed just before the first bounce is determined by free fall from the height $h$, so $h_1=e^2h$; then, falling from $h_1$ and bouncing a second time gives $h_2=e^2h_1$, and so on, so that

$$ h_n=e^{2n}h $$

holds. We have $h_1=0.80^2\times1.00=0.64\ \mathrm m$ (consistent with the statement of the problem) and $h_2=0.80^4\times1.00=0.4096\ \mathrm m\approx0.41\ \mathrm m$.

Exercise 2.8: Angular momentum of a particle and a central force

Solution

(1) The momentum is $\bm p=m\bm v=1.5\times(0,2,0)=(0,3,0)\ \mathrm{kg\,m/s}$. Applying the component expression of the cross product $\bm a\times\bm b=(a_yb_z-a_zb_y,\ a_zb_x-a_xb_z,\ a_xb_y-a_yb_x)$ to $\bm r=(2,0,0),\ \bm p=(0,3,0)$, we obtain

$$ \bm L=\bm r\times\bm p=\big(0\times0-0\times3,\ 0\times0-2\times0,\ 2\times3-0\times0\big)=(0,\,0,\,6)\ \mathrm{kg\,m^2/s} $$

(If we turn the fingers of the right hand from the $x$ axis toward the $y$ axis, the thumb points in the positive direction of the $z$ axis, so the $z$ component is positive.)

(2) Since $\bm r=(2,0,0)$ and $\bm F=(-4,0,0)$ are parallel, the torque is

$$ \bm N=\bm r\times\bm F=\big(0\times0-0\times0,\ 0\times(-4)-2\times0,\ 2\times0-0\times(-4)\big)=(0,0,0)=\bm 0 $$

By Law 2.5, $\dd\bm L/\dd t=\bm N=\bm 0$. If the force $\bm F$ is parallel to the position vector $\bm r$ (a central force directed toward the origin), $\bm N=\bm r\times\bm F=\bm 0$ always holds, so the angular momentum $\bm L$ is kept constant in time (Exercise 2.4). The value $\bm L=(0,0,6)\ \mathrm{kg\,m^2/s}$ at this instant is conserved and stays the same from then on.

← Back to Chapter 2, “Newton's Laws of Motion — Momentum and Angular Momentum”

Chapter 3 Description of Curvilinear Motion — Polar Coordinates and Tangential and Normal Acceleration

Exercise 3.1: A general formula for the radius of curvature $\rho(t)$

Solution

Differentiating each component, we find that the velocity and acceleration of the curve $\bm r(t)=[t,\ t^2,\ 0]$ are

$$ \bm v(t)=[1,\ 2t,\ 0],\qquad \bm a(t)=[0,\ 2,\ 0] $$

(the acceleration is constant, independent of the time $t$). The speed is

$$ v(t)=\abs{\bm v(t)}=\sqrt{1^2+(2t)^2}=\sqrt{1+4t^2} $$

To use the formula for the radius of curvature (Formula 3.3 of Chapter 3), $\rho=v^3/\abs{\bm v\times\bm a}$, we first compute $\bm v\times\bm a$. Since this is planar motion (the $z$ component is always 0), the cross product has only a $z$ component, whose value is $v_xa_y-v_ya_x$:

$$ \bm v\times\bm a=[\,0,\ 0,\ (1)(2)-(2t)(0)\,]=[0,\ 0,\ 2] \qquad\therefore\quad \abs{\bm v\times\bm a}=2 $$

(That $\abs{\bm v\times\bm a}$ turned out to be constant, independent of $t$, is due to a circumstance peculiar to this curve: the acceleration $\bm a$ is constant, and the $x$ component of the velocity is also constant.) Substituting into Formula 3.3 gives

$$ \rho(t)=\frac{v(t)^3}{\abs{\bm v\times\bm a}}=\frac{(1+4t^2)^{3/2}}{2} $$

which is the required general formula.

Check at $t=1$: $\rho(1)=\dfrac{(1+4)^{3/2}}{2}=\dfrac{5^{3/2}}{2}=\dfrac{5\sqrt5}{2}$, which agrees exactly with $\rho=5\sqrt5/2\ \mathrm m$ found in Example 3.4 of Chapter 3 (by the method of decomposing the acceleration into tangential and normal components). Thus two independent methods (the method of decomposition into components and the method of the cross-product formula) have reached the same answer for the same curve and the same point.

Exercise 3.2: A car rounding a curve

Solution

First we convert the units to $\mathrm{m/s}$:

$$ 72\ \mathrm{km/h}=\frac{72\times1000\ \mathrm m}{3600\ \mathrm s}=20\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

Since the radius of curvature of a circular-arc curve equals the radius of the circle (Section 3.4), $\rho=50\ \mathrm m$.

(a) By Theorem 3.2 of Chapter 3, the magnitude of the normal acceleration is

$$ \frac{v^2}{\rho}=\frac{(20\ \mathrm{m/s})^2}{50\ \mathrm m}=\frac{400}{50}\ \mathrm{m/s^2}=8.0\ \mathrm{m/s^2} $$

(the unit is $\mathrm{(m/s)^2/m=m/s^2}$, which agrees with the unit of acceleration). Its direction is toward the center of the curve.

(b) Let the mass of the car be $m$. The centripetal force needed to go around the curve is $m v^2/\rho$ (Newton's second law), and this is supplied by the static friction from the horizontal road surface. The maximum static friction is $\mu mg$, so the condition for not skidding sideways is

$$ \frac{mv^2}{\rho}\le\mu mg \qquad\therefore\quad \mu\ge\frac{v^2}{\rho g}=\frac{8.0}{9.8}\approx0.82 $$

(the mass $m$ cancels from both sides and disappears; that is, the condition is the same for a light car and for a heavy car). Therefore, unless the coefficient of static friction is at least about $0.82$, the car skids sideways. If the speed doubles, $v^2$ becomes four times as large, so the required $\mu$ also becomes four times as large.

(c) Since the car is decelerating, the tangential acceleration is the rate of change of the speed, $\dd v/\dd t=-2.0\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$ (opposite to the direction of travel). The normal acceleration is the $8.0\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$ of (a), and since the tangential and normal components are perpendicular to each other, their magnitudes can be combined by the Pythagorean theorem:

$$ \abs{\bm a}=\sqrt{\left(\frac{\dd v}{\dd t}\right)^2+\left(\frac{v^2}{\rho}\right)^2}=\sqrt{(-2.0)^2+8.0^2}\ \mathrm{m/s^2}=\sqrt{68}\ \mathrm{m/s^2}\approx8.2\ \mathrm{m/s^2} $$

Even with the deceleration, the normal component accounts for most of the magnitude of the acceleration.

Exercise 3.3: Derivatives of $\bm e_r,\bm e_\theta$ when $\theta$ varies with time

Solution

Since $\bm e_x,\bm e_y$ are constant vectors independent of time, when we differentiate $\bm e_r=\cos\theta\,\bm e_x+\sin\theta\,\bm e_y$ with respect to time, only the coefficients $\cos\theta,\sin\theta$ are differentiated. By the rule for differentiating a composite function, $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}\cos\theta=-\dot\theta\sin\theta$ and $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}\sin\theta=\dot\theta\cos\theta$, so

$$ \frac{\dd\bm e_r}{\dd t}=-\dot\theta\sin\theta\,\bm e_x+\dot\theta\cos\theta\,\bm e_y=\dot\theta\,(-\sin\theta\,\bm e_x+\cos\theta\,\bm e_y)=\dot\theta\,\bm e_\theta $$

(the last equality is the definition of $\bm e_\theta$, Formula 3.2 of Chapter 3). Similarly, differentiating $\bm e_\theta=-\sin\theta\,\bm e_x+\cos\theta\,\bm e_y$ gives

$$ \frac{\dd\bm e_\theta}{\dd t}=-\dot\theta\cos\theta\,\bm e_x-\dot\theta\sin\theta\,\bm e_y=-\dot\theta\,(\cos\theta\,\bm e_x+\sin\theta\,\bm e_y)=-\dot\theta\,\bm e_r $$

as required.

Next, we differentiate $\bm r=R\,\bm e_r$ ($R$ is constant) with respect to time. Since $R$ is a constant, it can be taken outside, and using $\dot\theta=\omega$ we obtain

$$ \bm v=\frac{\dd\bm r}{\dd t}=R\,\frac{\dd\bm e_r}{\dd t}=R\dot\theta\,\bm e_\theta=R\omega\,\bm e_\theta $$

The velocity points in the direction of $\bm e_\theta$ (the tangential direction of the circle), and the speed is $R\omega$. This also agrees with the Outlook in Section 3.4 (for uniform circular motion, $\bm e_t=\bm e_\theta$ and $\dd\bm e_t/\dd t=-\omega\bm e_r$, so that $\bm e_n=-\bm e_r$).

Exercise 3.4: A general proof of the orthogonality of the polar basis vectors

Solution

By Formula 3.2 of Chapter 3, the components of the polar-coordinate basis vectors are

$$ \bm e_r=[\cos\theta,\ \sin\theta],\qquad \bm e_\theta=[-\sin\theta,\ \cos\theta] $$

The inner product (dot product) is computed as the sum of the products of corresponding components (the definition of the inner product in University Mathematics, Section 15.2):

$$ \bm e_r\cdot\bm e_\theta=(\cos\theta)\times(-\sin\theta)+(\sin\theta)\times(\cos\theta) $$

Expanding the right-hand side gives

$$ =-\sin\theta\cos\theta+\sin\theta\cos\theta $$

and since the same term $\sin\theta\cos\theta$ appears exactly twice with opposite signs, these cancel each other, and

$$ \bm e_r\cdot\bm e_\theta=0 $$

This calculation uses no specific value of the angle $\theta$ at all (all the terms containing $\theta$ cancelled), so it always holds for any value of $\theta$—that is, we have shown that the polar-coordinate basis vectors $\bm e_r,\bm e_\theta$ are always orthogonal to each other, regardless of the position of the point (the angle $\theta$).

Exercise 3.5: The radius of curvature of $y=\sin x$

Solution

Let $f(x)=\sin x$. Then

$$ f'(x)=\cos x,\qquad f''(x)=-\sin x $$

Substituting into Formula 3.4 of Chapter 3, $\rho=(1+f'(x)^2)^{3/2}/\abs{f''(x)}$, we obtain

$$ \rho(x)=\frac{(1+\cos^2x)^{3/2}}{\abs{-\sin x}}=\frac{(1+\cos^2x)^{3/2}}{\abs{\sin x}} $$

which is the required general formula (we used $\abs{-\sin x}=\abs{\sin x}$).

Value at $x=\pi/2$ (the top of a peak): Substituting $\cos(\pi/2)=0,\ \sin(\pi/2)=1$, we get

$$ \rho\left(\frac\pi2\right)=\frac{(1+0^2)^{3/2}}{\abs{1}}=\frac{1^{3/2}}{1}=1 $$

That is, at the top of a peak (or the bottom of a trough) of the graph of $y=\sin x$, the radius of curvature is exactly 1. This means that near the top of a peak, the graph of $y=\sin x$ overlaps best with a circle of radius 1 (this is also convincing if we use the Taylor expansion $y=1-\dfrac{(x-\pi/2)^2}{2}+\cdots$, which shows that the behavior of $y=\sin x$ near $x=\pi/2$ has almost the same shape as the neighborhood of the top of a circle of radius 1).

Exercise 3.6: Velocity and acceleration at $t=0$ in helical motion

Solution

The velocity and acceleration of the helical motion $\bm r(t)=[\cos t,\ \sin t,\ t]$ of Example 3.6 of Chapter 3 were

$$ \bm v(t)=[-\sin t,\ \cos t,\ 1],\qquad \bm a(t)=[-\cos t,\ -\sin t,\ 0] $$

Substituting $t=0$ ($\sin0=0,\ \cos0=1$), we obtain

$$ \bm v(0)=[-\sin0,\ \cos0,\ 1]=[0,\ 1,\ 1],\qquad \bm a(0)=[-\cos0,\ -\sin0,\ 0]=[-1,\ 0,\ 0] $$

The speed is $v(0)=\abs{\bm v(0)}=\sqrt{0^2+1^2+1^2}=\sqrt2$. The components of the cross product are

$$ \bm v(0)\times\bm a(0)=[\,(1)(0)-(1)(0),\ \ (1)(-1)-(0)(0),\ \ (0)(0)-(1)(-1)\,]=[0,\ -1,\ 1] $$

and $\abs{\bm v(0)\times\bm a(0)}=\sqrt{0^2+(-1)^2+1^2}=\sqrt2$. By Formula 3.3,

$$ \rho(0)=\frac{v(0)^3}{\abs{\bm v(0)\times\bm a(0)}}=\frac{(\sqrt2)^3}{\sqrt2}=\frac{2\sqrt2}{\sqrt2}=2 $$

and we have confirmed that this indeed agrees, without contradiction even at the specific time $t=0$, with the result “$\rho=2$ (constant, independent of the time $t$)” found in Example 3.6.

← Back to Chapter 3, “Description of Curvilinear Motion — Polar Coordinates and Tangential and Normal Acceleration”

Chapter 4 Work and Energy

Exercise 4.1: Intuition for the inner product and work

Solution

Recall the definition of the inner product (dot product) $\bm a\cdot\bm b=\abs{\bm a}\abs{\bm b}\cos\theta$ ($\theta$ is the angle between the two vectors $\bm a,\bm b$). Since we assume $\bm a=\bm F\neq\bm0$ and $\bm b=\dd\bm r\neq\bm0$, we have $\abs{\bm a}\neq0$ and $\abs{\bm b}\neq0$. Therefore, the product $\abs{\bm a}\abs{\bm b}\cos\theta$ is 0 only when the remaining factor $\cos\theta$ is 0, that is, only when $\theta=90^\circ$ ($\bm F$ and $\dd\bm r$ are perpendicular to each other).

In other words, a force does no work when its direction is exactly perpendicular to the direction in which the particle moves (the direction of the displacement). This is because, no matter how large the force is, its component perpendicular to the displacement has no influence whatsoever on the “direction” of the motion (the intuition of Section 4.1 of the main text: the inner product measures the “magnitude of the influence” of the force in the direction of the displacement, and the influence of the perpendicular component is exactly 0).

We apply this result to the centripetal force in uniform circular motion. The centripetal force is a force that always points toward the center of the circle. On the other hand, the velocity of a particle in uniform circular motion (that is, the direction of the displacement $\dd\bm r/\dd t$) points along the tangent to the circle at the particle's position at that instant. The radial direction of the circle (the direction of the centripetal force, which points toward the center) and the tangential direction are always orthogonal geometrically (that the radius and the tangent meet at right angles is a basic property of the circle). Therefore, since the direction of the centripetal force and the direction of the displacement always make an angle of $90^\circ$, the centripetal force never does work during the motion—this is also the reason why the kinetic energy (and hence the speed) remains constant in uniform circular motion (the work–energy theorem, Theorem 4.1: if the work is 0, the change in kinetic energy is also 0).

Exercise 4.2: Determining whether a given force is conservative

Solution

(1) For $\bm F=[F_x,F_y,F_z]=[2xy,\ x^2,\ 0]$, we compute each component of the defining formula of the curl (Section 4.4.2). Since $F_z=0$ does not depend on $x,y$, the first and second components are immediately seen to be 0:

$$ \pdiff{F_z}{y}-\pdiff{F_y}{z}=0-0=0, \qquad \pdiff{F_x}{z}-\pdiff{F_z}{x}=0-0=0 $$

The third component is

$$ \pdiff{F_y}{x}-\pdiff{F_x}{y}=\pdiff{}{x}(x^2)-\pdiff{}{y}(2xy)=2x-2x=0 $$

Therefore $\rot\bm F=[0,0,0]=\bm0$, and by Theorem 4.2, $\bm F$ is a conservative force.

(2) From $\bm F=-\nabla U$ we have $F_x=-\pdiff Ux=2xy$, so $\pdiff Ux=-2xy$. Integrating this with respect to $x$ (we integrate regarding $y$ as a constant, so the constant of integration is in general a function $g(y)$ of $y$), we obtain

$$ U=-x^2y+g(y) $$

Next, we determine $g(y)$ by using the fact that this $U$ must also satisfy $F_y=-\pdiff Uy=x^2$. Differentiating $U=-x^2y+g(y)$ partially with respect to $y$ gives $\pdiff Uy=-x^2+g'(y)$. On the other hand, from $F_y=-\pdiff Uy=x^2$ we must have $\pdiff Uy=-x^2$. Comparing the two,

$$ -x^2+g'(y)=-x^2 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad g'(y)=0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad g(y)=(\text{constant}) $$

Taking the constant to be 0, we get

$$ U(x,y)=-x^2y $$

As a check, $-\pdiff{}{x}(-x^2y)=2xy=F_x$ and $-\pdiff{}{y}(-x^2y)=x^2=F_y$, so indeed $\bm F=-\nabla U$ is satisfied.

Exercise 4.3: Work done by friction on a parabola, and dependence on the path

Solution

(1)(i) The general formula shown in the “Discussion” of Section 4.3 says that when a particle is pulled slowly by a tangential force along a curve $y=f(x)$ ($0\le x\le X$) (a path along which $x$ only increases), the work done by the frictional force is

$$ W_{\text{friction}}=-\mu mgX $$

($X$ is the horizontal distance traveled; it does not depend on the shape of the curve in between). For $y=x^2\ (0\le x\le1)$ the horizontal distance is $X=1$, so simply substituting this gives

$$ W_{\text{friction}}=-\mu mg\times1=-\mu mg $$

as the work done by the frictional force.

(ii) We compute directly. The unit tangent vector is $\bm e_t=[1,f'(x)]/\sqrt{1+f'(x)^2}$, and since $f(x)=x^2$ we have $f'(x)=2x$, so

$$ \bm e_t=\frac{[1,2x]}{\sqrt{1+4x^2}} $$

From the equilibrium in the normal direction (the particle is pulled by a tangential force, so the normal component of gravity and the normal force balance each other), by the same derivation as in Illustration 4.1 of the main text, the magnitude of the normal force is $N=mg/\sqrt{1+f'(x)^2}=mg/\sqrt{1+4x^2}$, the kinetic friction is $\bm F_2=-\mu N\bm e_t$, and the arc-length element is $\dd s=\sqrt{1+4x^2}\,\dd x$. Since $\dd\bm r=\bm e_t\,\dd s$, the work done by the frictional force is

$$ W_{\text{friction}}=\int_0^1\bm F_2\cdot\bm e_t\,\dd s=\int_0^1(-\mu N)\,\dd s=-\int_0^1\mu\cdot\frac{mg}{\sqrt{1+4x^2}}\cdot\sqrt{1+4x^2}\,\dd x=-\int_0^1\mu mg\,\dd x=-\mu mg $$

(that the square-root factor cancels between the numerator and the denominator is exactly the same mechanism as in the generalization of Illustration 4.1). In both (i) and (ii) we obtain $W_{\text{friction}}=-\mu mg$, and the two results agree.

(2) The work done by friction while the particle is pulled from $x=0$ to $x=1$ is $-\mu mg\cdot1$ by (1). While it is pulled back ($x$ decreasing from $1$ to $0.5$), the kinetic friction is again directed opposite to the motion, and its magnitude is $\mu N$. Writing $\dd\bm r=\bm e_t'\,\dd s$ in this case ($\bm e_t'$ is the unit vector in the direction of travel, which is now the direction in which $x$ decreases), we have $\bm F_2=-\mu N\bm e_t'$, and $\bm F_2\cdot\dd\bm r=-\mu N\,\dd s$ is unchanged. Since the increment of the arc length $s$ is the positive quantity $\dd s=\sqrt{1+4x^2}\,\abs{\dd x}$,

$$ W_{\text{pull-back}}=-\int\mu N\,\dd s=-\int_{0.5}^{1}\mu\frac{mg}{\sqrt{1+4x^2}}\sqrt{1+4x^2}\,\dd x=-\mu mg(1-0.5)=-0.5\,\mu mg $$

(when integrating over $x$ from $1$ to $0.5$, we use $\abs{\dd x}$ so that the length $0.5$ of the interval of integration is taken as positive). Therefore the work done by friction over the whole trip is

$$ W_{\text{total}}=-\mu mg-0.5\,\mu mg=-1.5\,\mu mg $$

On the other hand, if the particle is pulled directly from $x=0$ to $x=0.5$, the general formula of (1) with $X=0.5$ gives $W=-0.5\,\mu mg$. Although the starting point $x=0$ and the endpoint $x=0.5$ are the same for both paths, the work done by friction differs: $-1.5\,\mu mg$ and $-0.5\,\mu mg$. Since the work done by a conservative force must be determined by the starting point and the endpoint alone, independent of the path ($W_c=U(P_1)-U(P_2)$ in Section 4.4.1), the frictional force is not a conservative force. (For a path along which $x$ only increases, the work is determined by the horizontal distance $X$ alone, but if $x$ goes back and forth, the horizontal distance covered in the round trip is added.)

Exercise 4.4: Work and energy for an object sliding down an incline

Solution

(1) Take the direction in which the object slides down along the incline as positive. The component of gravity along the incline is $mg\sin\alpha$ (in the sliding direction, positive), and the object moves a distance $L$, so by $W=\bm F\cdot\Delta\bm r$ of Section 4.1 (for straight-line motion),

$$ W_{\text{gravity}}=mg\sin\alpha\times L=mgL\sin\alpha $$

(positive work: gravity helps the motion along). The magnitude of the normal force is $N=mg\cos\alpha$ from the equilibrium of forces perpendicular to the incline, and the magnitude of the kinetic friction is $\mu N=\mu mg\cos\alpha$, directed so as to oppose the motion (opposite to the direction of sliding down the incline). Therefore,

$$ W_{\text{friction}}=-\mu mg\cos\alpha\times L=-\mu mgL\cos\alpha $$

(negative work).

(2) The object started to slide gently (initial speed 0), so the initial kinetic energy is $K_1=0$. The work done by the net force is the sum of the work done by gravity, the frictional force, and the normal force (the normal force does no work because it is perpendicular to the direction of motion), so $W=W_{\text{gravity}}+W_{\text{friction}}$. By the work–energy theorem (Theorem 4.1),

$$ W_{\text{gravity}}+W_{\text{friction}}=K_2-K_1=\frac12mv^2-0 $$

Solving this for $v$, we obtain

$$ v=\sqrt{\frac{2(W_{\text{gravity}}+W_{\text{friction}})}{m}}=\sqrt{\frac{2\left(mgL\sin\alpha-\mu mgL\cos\alpha\right)}{m}}=\sqrt{2gL\left(\sin\alpha-\mu\cos\alpha\right)} $$

(Note that the mass $m$ cancels out. This result is meaningful only when $\sin\alpha>\mu\cos\alpha$, that is, when $\tan\alpha>\mu$; in other words, only when the incline overcomes friction so that the object starts to slide at all.)

Exercise 4.5: Component form of the condition for a conservative force — three and two dimensions

Solution

From the defining formula of the curl (Section 4.4.2), writing $\rot\bm F=\bm0$ in components gives three simultaneous conditions:

$$ \pdiff{F_z}{y}-\pdiff{F_y}{z}=0,\qquad \pdiff{F_x}{z}-\pdiff{F_z}{x}=0,\qquad \pdiff{F_y}{x}-\pdiff{F_x}{y}=0 $$

This is the general form in three dimensions. Now consider a two-dimensional force for motion only in the $xy$ plane, that is, $F_z=0$ and $F_x,F_y$ independent of $z$ ($\pdiff{F_x}{z}=\pdiff{F_y}{z}=0$), and substitute these conditions into the three equations.

First equation: $\pdiff{F_z}{y}-\pdiff{F_y}{z}=\pdiff{}{y}(0)-0=0-0=0$—this is satisfied automatically because $F_z=0$ (a constant).

Second equation: $\pdiff{F_x}{z}-\pdiff{F_z}{x}=0-\pdiff{}{x}(0)=0-0=0$—this too is satisfied automatically.

Third equation: $\pdiff{F_y}{x}-\pdiff{F_x}{y}=0$—since $F_x,F_y$ remain as functions of $x,y$, this is not satisfied automatically, and it remains as a genuinely meaningful condition.

Therefore, for a two-dimensional force, the condition for being conservative simplifies to effectively just one of the three equations,

$$ \pdiff{F_y}{x}-\pdiff{F_x}{y}=0 \qquad\Bigl(\text{that is}\ \pdiff{F_y}{x}=\pdiff{F_x}{y}\Bigr) $$

This expresses the fact that, of the three-dimensional $\rot\bm F$, only the component perpendicular to the $xy$ plane (the $z$ component) is meaningful, and the other two components vanish identically in two-dimensional motion. (The condition used in Exercise 4.2 is this two-dimensional version (the third equation); in Example 4.4 we computed all three components.)

(3) For $\bm F=[y^2,\ 2xy,\ z]$, we compute the three components of $\rot\bm F$:

$$ \pdiff{F_z}{y}-\pdiff{F_y}{z}=0-0=0,\qquad \pdiff{F_x}{z}-\pdiff{F_z}{x}=0-0=0,\qquad \pdiff{F_y}{x}-\pdiff{F_x}{y}=2y-2y=0 $$

Hence $\rot\bm F=\bm0$, and by Theorem 4.2, $\bm F$ is a conservative force. We now find the potential. From $F_x=-\pdiff Ux=y^2$ we get $\pdiff Ux=-y^2$, and integrating with respect to $x$ gives $U=-xy^2+g(y,z)$ (the constant of integration is a function of $y,z$). Differentiating this partially with respect to $y$ gives $\pdiff Uy=-2xy+\pdiff gy$, and comparing with $F_y=-\pdiff Uy=2xy$, that is, $\pdiff Uy=-2xy$, we find $\pdiff gy=0$; that is, $g$ is a function $g(z)$ of $z$ only. Furthermore, from $F_z=-\pdiff Uz=z$ we have $\pdiff Uz=-z$, so $g'(z)=-z$ and $g(z)=-\dfrac{z^2}2$ (taking the constant to be 0). Therefore

$$ U(x,y,z)=-xy^2-\frac{z^2}2 $$

As a check, $-\pdiff Ux=y^2$, $-\pdiff Uy=2xy$, and $-\pdiff Uz=z$, which agree with $\bm F$.

Exercise 4.6: Stability of equilibrium on a potential energy curve

Solution

(1) The equilibrium points are the points that satisfy $\dd U/\dd x=0$. Differentiating $U(x)=\dfrac{x^4}{4}-\dfrac{x^2}{2}$, we obtain

$$ \diff Ux=x^3-x=x(x^2-1)=x(x-1)(x+1) $$

This vanishes at the three points $x=0,\ 1,\ -1$.

(2) To judge stability, we examine the sign of the second derivative, as in Theorem 4.4. We have $\dd^2U/\dd x^2=3x^2-1$.

  • $x=0$: $3(0)^2-1=-1\lt0$ ⟹ unstable equilibrium.
  • $x=1$: $3(1)^2-1=2\gt0$ ⟹ stable equilibrium.
  • $x=-1$: $3(-1)^2-1=2\gt0$ ⟹ stable equilibrium.

In other words, $U(x)$ has two left–right symmetric valleys (stable equilibria) at $x=\pm1$, and a hill (unstable equilibrium) at $x=0$ between them; it is a so-called “double-well” potential.

(3) By the idea of Section 4.6.2 ($U(x)\le E$), the range in which motion with mechanical energy $E=0$ is possible is the range of $x$ satisfying $U(x)\le0$. Since $U(x)=\dfrac{x^4}{4}-\dfrac{x^2}{2}=\dfrac{x^2}{4}\left(x^2-2\right)$ can be factored, $U(x)\le0$ holds when $x^2\le2$ (because $x^2/4\ge0$ always holds, the sign is determined by the factor $(x^2-2)$), that is,

$$ -\sqrt2\ \le\ x\ \le\ \sqrt2 $$

This is the energetically allowed region (the range satisfying $U(x)\le E$). The turning points for $E=0$ are $x=\pm\sqrt2$ ($U=E$ and $U'=x^3-x\neq0$; Definition 4.5).

However, a particle does not necessarily reach every part of the allowed region. At $x=0$ we have $U(0)=0=E$, and moreover $U'(0)=0$ and $U''(0)=-1\lt0$, so this is an unstable equilibrium point (the top of the hill) and not a turning point. Near $x=0$, the speed of a particle with $E=0$ approaches $0$, and the particle merely approaches $x=0$ over an infinitely long time and cannot pass through that point in a finite time (asymptotic approach to an unstable equilibrium point). Therefore, the range in which a particle starting from the valley at $x\gt0$ can actually move is $0\lt x\le\sqrt2$, and for a particle starting from the valley at $x\lt0$ it is limited to $-\sqrt2\le x\lt0$. If $E$ exceeds $0$ even slightly, the particle can cross $x=0$ and go back and forth between the left and right valleys. $E=0$ is at the same height as the top of the central hill, and it marks the boundary between motion that “crosses between the wells” and motion that is “confined to one of the wells.”

Exercise 4.7: Work done by a swirling force and the curl

Solution

For $\bm F=[-y,\ x,\ 0]$ we have $\bm F\cdot\dd\bm r=-y\,\dd x+x\,\dd y$. We compute the four sides of the square in order.

  • Side 1: $(1,-1)\to(1,1)$. Here $x=1$ is constant, $\dd x=0$, and $y$ goes from $-1$ to $1$. Since $\bm F\cdot\dd\bm r=x\,\dd y=\dd y$, we have $\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\dd y=2$.
  • Side 2: $(1,1)\to(-1,1)$. Here $y=1$ is constant, $\dd y=0$, and $x$ goes from $1$ to $-1$. Since $\bm F\cdot\dd\bm r=-y\,\dd x=-\dd x$, we have $\displaystyle\int_{1}^{-1}(-\dd x)=-(-1-1)=2$.
  • Side 3: $(-1,1)\to(-1,-1)$. Here $x=-1$ is constant, and $y$ goes from $1$ to $-1$. Since $\bm F\cdot\dd\bm r=x\,\dd y=-\dd y$, we have $\displaystyle\int_{1}^{-1}(-\dd y)=2$.
  • Side 4: $(-1,-1)\to(1,-1)$. Here $y=-1$ is constant, and $x$ goes from $-1$ to $1$. Since $\bm F\cdot\dd\bm r=-y\,\dd x=\dd x$, we have $\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\dd x=2$.

Therefore, the work around one loop is

$$ \oint\bm F\cdot\dd\bm r=2+2+2+2=8\neq0 $$

On the other hand, by the result of Example 4.7, the third component of $\rot\bm F$ is $\pdiff{F_y}{x}-\pdiff{F_x}{y}=2$, and the area of the square is $2\times2=4$, so their product is $2\times4=8$, which exactly coincides with the work around one loop (this is a concrete example of Stokes's theorem (University Mathematics, Section 20.5), “the line integral around a closed path equals the surface integral of the curl”). Since the work around one loop is not 0, and for a conservative force the work around one loop would have to be 0, this force is not conservative.

← Back to Chapter 4, “Work and Energy”

Chapter 5 Projectile Motion and Circular Motion — Air Resistance and Centripetal Acceleration

Exercise 5.1: Symmetry of the horizontal range, and the time of reaching the highest point and its height

Solution

(1) Symmetry of the range By the result of Section 5.1, the horizontal range of projectile motion without air resistance is, for initial speed $v_0$ and launch angle $\theta$,

$$ R(\theta)=\frac{v_0^2\sin2\theta}{g} $$

If the launch angle is changed to $90^\circ-\theta$, then $2(90^\circ-\theta)=180^\circ-2\theta$, so using $\sin(180^\circ-x)=\sin x$ (on the unit circle, the points at angles $x$ and $180^\circ-x$ have equal $y$ coordinates), we obtain

$$ R(90^\circ-\theta)=\frac{v_0^2\sin(180^\circ-2\theta)}{g}=\frac{v_0^2\sin2\theta}{g}=R(\theta) $$

Hence the horizontal range is the same for $\theta$ and $90^\circ-\theta$ ($\theta=45^\circ$ is its own partner, since $90^\circ-45^\circ=45^\circ$, so it has no separate pair; it is the launch angle at which $\sin2\theta$ takes its maximum value 1).

(2) Time and height of the highest point The vertical velocity in projectile motion without air resistance is given by the general expression obtained by integrating the equation of motion (Section 5.1):

$$ v_y(t)=v_0\sin\theta-gt $$

The highest point is the instant at which the ball turns from rising to falling, that is, the time at which the vertical velocity is exactly 0. So we set $v_y(t)=0$ and solve for $t$:

$$ v_0\sin\theta-gt_{\text{top}}=0 \qquad\therefore\ t_{\text{top}}=\frac{v_0\sin\theta}{g} $$

Substituting $v_0=15\ \mathrm{m/s}$, $\theta=45^\circ$ ($\sin45^\circ=\dfrac{\sqrt2}{2}\approx0.7071$), and $g=9.8\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$ gives

$$ t_{\text{top}}=\frac{15\times0.7071}{9.8}=\frac{10.607}{9.8}\approx1.082\ \mathrm{s} $$

Next, the height $H$ of the highest point is found by substituting $t=t_{\text{top}}$ into the expression for the vertical position, $y(t)=v_0t\sin\theta-\frac12gt^2$ (the same procedure as in the derivation in Section 5.1):

$$ H=v_0t_{\text{top}}\sin\theta-\frac12g\,t_{\text{top}}^2 =\frac{v_0^2\sin^2\theta}{g}-\frac12\cdot\frac{v_0^2\sin^2\theta}{g} =\frac{v_0^2\sin^2\theta}{2g} $$

Substituting the numerical values gives

$$ H=\frac{15^2\times0.7071^2}{2\times9.8}=\frac{225\times0.5}{19.6}=\frac{112.5}{19.6}\approx5.740\ \mathrm{m} $$

Therefore, the time at which the highest point is reached is about $1.082\ \mathrm s$, and the height at that moment is about $5.740\ \mathrm m$.

Exercise 5.2: The physical meaning of terminal velocity

Solution

Under air resistance proportional to the velocity, the vertical velocity is given by (Section 5.2)

$$ v_y(t)=\Big(v_{0y}+\frac{g}{b}\Big)e^{-bt}-\frac{g}{b} $$

We consider the limit $t\to\infty$. Since $b\gt0$, the exponential function $e^{-bt}$ approaches 0 as $t\to\infty$ (for any initial velocity $v_{0y}$, the coefficient $\big(v_{0y}+\dfrac gb\big)$ is a finite constant, so the whole term obtained by multiplying it by $e^{-bt}$, which approaches 0, also approaches 0). Therefore,

$$ \lim_{t\to\infty}v_y(t)=\underbrace{\Big(v_{0y}+\frac gb\Big)\times0}_{\to\,0}-\frac gb=-\frac gb $$

Thus the limiting value $-g/b$ is not affected at all by the initial velocity $v_{0y}$ with which the object was thrown (whether large or small, upward or downward)—the information about the initial velocity is “forgotten” through the decay $e^{-bt}\to0$.

The physical meaning of this can be understood from the balance of forces. The object is acted on by gravity $mg$ downward and by air resistance $B|v_y|$ in the direction opposite to the velocity. When the object has accelerated enough and its falling speed has become large, the air resistance also grows, and eventually it balances gravity exactly:

$$ mg=B|v_y| \qquad\therefore\ |v_y|=\frac{mg}{B}=\frac gb $$

This speed, at which the net force (and hence the acceleration) becomes 0, is the vertically downward terminal velocity (Definition 5.1). However the initial velocity is chosen, once enough time has passed the object always settles into this state of force balance (the terminal velocity), so it is only natural that the limit as $t\to\infty$ does not depend on the initial velocity. (If the initial velocity is already faster than the terminal velocity, the resistance is larger than gravity, the object decelerates, and again approaches the same terminal velocity.)

Exercise 5.3: Two ways to confirm the centripetal acceleration $v^2/r=r\omega^2$

Solution

(1) From the second derivative of the position vector (the method of Section 5.4 of this chapter)

The position vector of uniform circular motion with radius $r$ and (constant) angular velocity $\omega$ is $\bm r(t)=[r\cos\omega t,\,r\sin\omega t]$, and differentiating it twice (the derivation in Section 5.4) gives the acceleration

$$ \bm a(t)=-\omega^2\bm r(t) $$

Its magnitude is $|\bm a|=\omega^2|\bm r(t)|=r\omega^2$ ($|\bm r(t)|=r$ is constant). On the other hand, the magnitude of the velocity (the speed) is $v=|\bm v|=r\omega$, since $\bm v(t)=\dot{\bm r}(t)=r\omega[-\sin\omega t,\cos\omega t]$, so substituting $\omega=v/r$ into $|\bm a|=r\omega^2$ gives

$$ |\bm a|=r\left(\frac vr\right)^2=\frac{v^2}{r} $$

Thus we obtain $|\bm a|=v^2/r=r\omega^2$.

(2) From the decomposition into tangential and normal components (the method of Chapter 3)

In Chapter 3 we learned that the acceleration in general curvilinear motion can be decomposed into a component along the direction of motion (the tangential direction, unit vector $\bm e_t$) and a component in the direction perpendicular to it (the principal normal direction, unit vector $\bm e_n$, pointing to the side toward which the curve bends):

$$ \bm a=\dot v\,\bm e_t+\frac{v^2}{\rho}\,\bm e_n $$

($v$ is the speed and $\rho$ is the radius of curvature). In uniform circular motion, the speed $v=r\omega$ is constant in time, so the tangential component is $\dot v=0$. Also, for motion on a circle, the bending of the curve (the circle) is characterized by the radius of the circle itself, so the radius of curvature is $\rho=r$. Substituting these, we obtain

$$ \bm a=0\cdot\bm e_t+\frac{v^2}{r}\,\bm e_n=\frac{v^2}{r}\,\bm e_n $$

Thus the magnitude of the acceleration is $|\bm a|=v^2/r$, and its direction is the principal normal direction $\bm e_n$ (the direction toward the center of the circle). This is exactly the same result, in both magnitude and direction, as the centripetal acceleration found in (1), and substituting $v=r\omega$ again gives $v^2/r=r\omega^2$.

The two approaches—(1) differentiating the position vector twice as a vector in its rectangular (Cartesian) components, and (2) decomposing the velocity and acceleration into the tangential and normal directions along the “direction” of the motion—differ in their starting points and in their computational tools, but they reach the same conclusion about the same physical phenomenon (the centripetal acceleration of uniform circular motion). This is one concrete example of the fact that physical laws do not depend on the choice of coordinate system.

Exercise 5.4: Orbital speed of an artificial satellite

Solution

(1) Orbital speed in symbols When an artificial satellite (mass $m$) moves in uniform circular motion in a circular orbit of radius $r$ around the Earth (mass $M$), the only force acting on the satellite is the gravitational attraction from the Earth. By Newton's law of universal gravitation, its magnitude is

$$ F_{\text{grav}}=\frac{GMm}{r^2} $$

and its direction always points toward the center of the Earth (the center of the circular orbit). On the other hand, the magnitude of the centripetal force required for this satellite to move in uniform circular motion of radius $r$ and speed $v$ is obtained by rewriting the centripetal-force expression of Chapter 5 (Definition 5.2), $F=m\omega^2r$ (the form with angular velocity $\omega$), using the speed $v=r\omega$ (that is, $\omega=v/r$): $F=m(v/r)^2r=mv^2/r$. Hence

$$ F_{\text{centripetal}}=\frac{mv^2}{r} $$

The force that keeps the satellite in its circular orbit is precisely this gravitational attraction itself, so the two forces are equal:

$$ \frac{mv^2}{r}=\frac{GMm}{r^2} $$

Dividing both sides by the mass $m$ (the satellite's own mass; since $m$ appears in the same form in both the gravitational-force expression and the centripetal-force expression, it cancels), we obtain

$$ \frac{v^2}{r}=\frac{GM}{r^2} \qquad\therefore\ v^2=\frac{GM}{r} $$

Choosing $v\gt0$ and taking the square root, the orbital speed is

$$ v=\sqrt{\frac{GM}{r}} $$

The fact that the satellite's own mass $m$ does not appear in the final answer is an important result: it means that once the orbital radius $r$ is fixed, the satellite circles at the same speed however heavy it is (regardless of its mass). (This is for the same reason that the acceleration of free fall does not depend on mass—both the gravitational attraction and the two sides of the equation of motion are proportional to the mass $m$.)

(2) Numerical values Substitute $G=6.674\,30\times10^{-11}\ \mathrm{N\,m^2/kg^2}$, $M=5.972\times10^{24}\ \mathrm{kg}$, and $r=6.371\times10^{6}\ \mathrm m$ into $v=\sqrt{GM/r}$. First, $GM=6.674\,30\times10^{-11}\times5.972\times10^{24}\approx3.986\times10^{14}\ \mathrm{N\,m^2/kg}=\mathrm{m^3/s^2}$, and

$$ v=\sqrt{\frac{3.986\times10^{14}}{6.371\times10^{6}}}=\sqrt{6.256\times10^{7}}\approx7.91\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{m/s}=7.91\ \mathrm{km/s} $$

(The units, $\mathrm{\sqrt{(m^3/s^2)/m}}=\mathrm{m/s}$, are correct.) The period is found by dividing the length of one revolution, $2\pi r$, by the speed $v$:

$$ T=\frac{2\pi r}{v}=\frac{2\pi\times6.371\times10^{6}}{7.91\times10^{3}}\approx5.06\times10^{3}\ \mathrm s\approx84\ \text{min} $$

Thus the speed of a circular orbit just skimming the Earth's surface is about $7.9\ \mathrm{km/s}$ (the first cosmic velocity), and the time for one revolution is about 84 minutes.

Exercise 5.5: Limit of the horizontal position in projectile motion with air resistance

Solution

Under air resistance proportional to the velocity, the horizontal position is given by (Section 5.2)

$$ x(t)=\frac{v_{0x}}{b}\big(1-e^{-bt}\big) $$

Taking the limit $t\to\infty$, since $e^{-bt}\to0$ ($b\gt0$), we have

$$ \lim_{t\to\infty}x(t)=\frac{v_{0x}}{b}\big(1-0\big)=\frac{v_{0x}}{b} $$

That is, it converges to a finite value. This is in contrast to the vertical position $y(t)$, which (because of the term $-\dfrac gb\,t$ in its expression) keeps decreasing without bound as $t\to\infty$.

The physical reason for this difference is that the horizontal and vertical directions differ in whether there is a “balancing force.” In the horizontal direction gravity does not act, and only air resistance acts (equation of motion $\dot v_x=-bv_x$). Therefore, the horizontal velocity approaches 0 exponentially with time, the object keeps decelerating indefinitely, and finally comes to a stop (its speed becomes small enough to be regarded as 0 for practical purposes). When the velocity approaches 0, the object scarcely advances any further horizontally, so the horizontal position approaches a particular value $v_{0x}/b$ and levels off. In the vertical direction, on the other hand, gravity keeps acting all the time, so the velocity (instead of approaching 0 as in the horizontal direction) approaches the terminal velocity $-g/b$, which is a finite constant value (it does not become 0). As long as the object keeps falling at the terminal velocity, the vertical position keeps decreasing forever (at a constant rate), so $y(t)$ diverges. To summarize: “because of air resistance, the horizontal motion eventually stops, but in the vertical direction the object is pulled by gravity and keeps falling (at the terminal velocity)”—this is the physical meaning of $x(t)$ converging while $y(t)$ diverges, and it is also consistent with the obvious fact that, even with air resistance, the object cannot avoid reaching the ground (or the water surface) itself.

Exercise 5.6: Cross-sectional area and terminal velocity

Solution

Under resistance proportional to the square of the velocity, the terminal velocity is given by (Formula 5.3)

$$ v_{\text t}=\sqrt{\frac{mg}{c}},\qquad c=\frac12C_{\mathrm d}\rho_{\text{air}}A $$

Using the same $m=2.0\ \mathrm{kg}$, $C_{\mathrm d}=1.05$, and $\rho_{\text{air}}=1.2\ \mathrm{kg/m^3}$ as in Example 5.5, and changing only the cross-sectional area to $A=0.50\ \mathrm{m^2}$ (twice the $A=0.25\ \mathrm{m^2}$ of Example 5.5), we recompute the drag coefficient:

$$ c=\frac12\times1.05\times1.2\times0.50=0.315\ \mathrm{kg/m} $$

This is exactly twice the $c\approx0.1575\ \mathrm{kg/m}$ of Example 5.5 (from the form $c=\frac12C_{\mathrm d}\rho_{\text{air}}A$ one can read off directly that doubling $A$ doubles $c$). Substituting this into the expression for the terminal velocity gives

$$ v_{\text t}=\sqrt{\frac{2.0\times9.8}{0.315}}=\sqrt{62.22}\approx7.89\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

How many times its former value the terminal velocity becomes when the cross-sectional area is doubled can be found without redoing the calculation, from the relations $c\propto A$ and $v_{\text t}\propto1/\sqrt c$ (proportionalities that can be seen from the form of the formulas): if $A$ doubles, $c$ also doubles, and $v_{\text t}$ is inversely proportional to the square root of the drag coefficient, so

$$ \frac{v_{\text{t,new}}}{v_{\text{t,old}}}=\sqrt{\frac{c_{\text{old}}}{c_{\text{new}}}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt2}\approx0.707 $$

That is, the terminal velocity becomes about 0.707 times its former value. Indeed, multiplying $v_{\text t}\approx11.16\ \mathrm{m/s}$ of Example 5.5 by $1/\sqrt2\approx0.707$ gives $11.16\times0.707\approx7.89\ \mathrm{m/s}$, which agrees with the value computed directly above. Physically, this agrees with the intuition that when a parachute is opened and the cross-sectional area is increased, the object experiences stronger air resistance even at the same speed, so the speed at which the resistance balances gravity (the terminal velocity) becomes smaller.

Exercise 5.7: Position and velocity under resistance

Solution

By Formula 5.2 of Section 5.2, the velocity and position in projectile motion under resistance proportional to the velocity are

$$ v_x=v_{0x}e^{-bt},\qquad v_y=\Big(v_{0y}+\frac gb\Big)e^{-bt}-\frac gb, $$ $$ x=\frac{v_{0x}}{b}\big(1-e^{-bt}\big),\qquad y=\frac1b\Big(v_{0y}+\frac gb\Big)\big(1-e^{-bt}\big)-\frac gb\,t $$

With $v_{0x}=12\ \mathrm{m/s}$, $v_{0y}=16\ \mathrm{m/s}$, $b=0.50\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, and $g=9.8\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$, we have $g/b=19.6\ \mathrm{m/s}$ and $v_{0y}+g/b=35.6\ \mathrm{m/s}$. At $t=1.0\ \mathrm s$, $bt=0.50$, $e^{-0.50}\approx0.6065$, and $1-e^{-0.50}\approx0.3935$, so

$$ \begin{aligned} x&=\frac{12}{0.50}\times0.3935=24\times0.3935\approx9.44\ \mathrm m,\\ y&=\frac{35.6}{0.50}\times0.3935-19.6\times1.0=71.2\times0.3935-19.6\approx28.02-19.6\approx8.42\ \mathrm m \end{aligned} $$

Also, the velocities are

$$ v_x=12\times0.6065\approx7.28\ \mathrm{m/s},\qquad v_y=35.6\times0.6065-19.6\approx21.59-19.6\approx1.99\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

(Since $v_y\gt0$, the object is still rising at $t=1.0\ \mathrm s$.) Without air resistance, the position at the same time is $x=v_{0x}t=12\ \mathrm m$, $y=v_{0y}t-\frac12gt^2=16-4.9=11.1\ \mathrm m$. Therefore, with resistance, the horizontal position decreases from $12\ \mathrm m$ to about $9.4\ \mathrm m$, and the vertical position from $11.1\ \mathrm m$ to about $8.4\ \mathrm m$; both become smaller.

← Back to Chapter 5, “Projectile Motion and Circular Motion — Air Resistance and Centripetal Acceleration”

Chapter 6 Motion in Polar Coordinates and Conic Sections

Exercise 6.1: Solving the basis-vector relation in reverse

Solution

In Chapter 6 we saw that the relation between the basis vectors can be written in matrix form as $[\bm e_r\ \bm e_\theta]=[\bm e_x\ \bm e_y]R(\theta)$ (where $R(\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{bmatrix}$). To solve this for $[\bm e_x\ \bm e_y]$, it suffices to multiply both sides by $R(\theta)^{-1}$ from the right:

$$ [\bm e_x\ \bm e_y]=[\bm e_r\ \bm e_\theta]\,R(\theta)^{-1} $$

As we confirmed in Section 6.1 (ii), the inverse matrix of a rotation matrix equals its transpose ($R(\theta)^{-1}=R(\theta)^{\mathrm T}=\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{bmatrix}$). Substituting this gives

$$ [\bm e_x\ \bm e_y]=[\bm e_r\ \bm e_\theta]\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{bmatrix} $$

Writing out this matrix product column by column (the first column gives $\bm e_x$, and the second column gives $\bm e_y$), we obtain

$$ \bm e_x=\cos\theta\,\bm e_r-\sin\theta\,\bm e_\theta,\qquad \bm e_y=\sin\theta\,\bm e_r+\cos\theta\,\bm e_\theta $$

This is the inverse relation we were looking for. As a check, if we substitute $\bm e_r=\cos\theta\bm e_x+\sin\theta\bm e_y,\ \bm e_\theta=-\sin\theta\bm e_x+\cos\theta\bm e_y$ into the right-hand side, we get $\bm e_x=\cos\theta(\cos\theta\bm e_x+\sin\theta\bm e_y)-\sin\theta(-\sin\theta\bm e_x+\cos\theta\bm e_y)=(\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta)\bm e_x+(\cos\theta\sin\theta-\sin\theta\cos\theta)\bm e_y=\bm e_x$, so it is indeed an identity (the same holds for $\bm e_y$).

Exercise 6.2: Perihelion and aphelion distances

Solution

All we need to do is substitute $\theta=0$ ($\cos0=1$) and $\theta=\pi$ ($\cos\pi=-1$) into Formula 6.3 of Chapter 6, $r(\theta)=\dfrac{k}{1+\varepsilon\cos\theta}$:

$$ r(0)=\frac{k}{1+\varepsilon\cos0}=\frac{k}{1+\varepsilon},\qquad r(\pi)=\frac{k}{1+\varepsilon\cos\pi}=\frac{k}{1-\varepsilon}\quad(0\le\varepsilon\lt1) $$

$r(0)$ is the distance to the point at $\theta=0$ (the point closest to the focal point, which is the perihelion in the planetary motion treated in Chapter 8), and $r(\pi)$ is the distance to the point farthest from the focal point (the aphelion). The larger $\varepsilon$ is, the smaller $r(0)$ and the larger $r(\pi)$ become—we see that the larger the eccentricity of an orbit, the more closely it approaches the focal point at perihelion and the farther away it goes at aphelion (as $\varepsilon\to1$, $r(\pi)\to\infty$, and the ellipse approaches an open parabola).

These two points are the two ends of the major axis, so the length of the major axis is $2a=r(0)+r(\pi)$:

$$ 2a=\frac{k}{1+\varepsilon}+\frac{k}{1-\varepsilon}=\frac{k(1-\varepsilon)+k(1+\varepsilon)}{(1+\varepsilon)(1-\varepsilon)}=\frac{2k}{1-\varepsilon^2} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad a=\frac{k}{1-\varepsilon^2} $$

This agrees with $a=k/(1-\varepsilon^2)$ in Theorem 6.1 of Chapter 6. We can also write $r(0)=a(1-\varepsilon)$ and $r(\pi)=a(1+\varepsilon)$ ($\dfrac{k}{1+\varepsilon}=\dfrac{k(1-\varepsilon)}{1-\varepsilon^2}$).

Exercise 6.3: An ellipse of eccentricity 0.8

Solution

Substituting $\varepsilon=0.8=\dfrac45,\ k=2$ into the general form $(1-\varepsilon^2)x^2+2k\varepsilon x+y^2-k^2=0$ in rectangular (Cartesian) coordinates derived in Chapter 6, we obtain

$$ \left(1-\frac{16}{25}\right)x^2+2\times2\times\frac45\,x+y^2-4=0 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \frac{9}{25}x^2+\frac{16}{5}x+y^2-4=0 $$

We complete the square in $x$. Since $\dfrac{9}{25}x^2+\dfrac{16}{5}x=\dfrac{9}{25}\left(x^2+\dfrac{16}{5}\times\dfrac{25}{9}x\right)=\dfrac{9}{25}\left(x^2+\dfrac{80}{9}x\right)=\dfrac{9}{25}\left[\left(x+\dfrac{40}{9}\right)^2-\left(\dfrac{40}{9}\right)^2\right]$,

$$ \frac{9}{25}\left(x+\frac{40}{9}\right)^2-\frac{9}{25}\times\frac{1600}{81}+y^2-4=0 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \frac{9}{25}\left(x+\frac{40}{9}\right)^2+y^2=4+\frac{1600}{225}=\frac{900+1600}{225}=\frac{100}{9} $$

Dividing both sides by $\dfrac{100}{9}$, we get

$$ \frac{\left(x+\dfrac{40}{9}\right)^2}{\dfrac{2500}{81}}+\frac{y^2}{\dfrac{100}{9}}=1 $$

This is the standard form of an ellipse with center $\left(-\dfrac{40}{9},0\right)$, semi-major axis $a=\dfrac{50}{9}\approx5.56$, and semi-minor axis $b=\dfrac{10}{3}\approx3.33$. As a check, the distance from the center to the focal point is $c=\sqrt{a^2-b^2}=\sqrt{\dfrac{2500}{81}-\dfrac{900}{81}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{1600}{81}}=\dfrac{40}{9}$, which coincides exactly with the distance from the center to the origin, $\left|-\dfrac{40}{9}\right|=\dfrac{40}{9}$—this is consistent with the fact that Formula 6.3 is constructed with the origin as the focal point.

Let us also compare with the values given by Theorem 6.1. We have $a=\dfrac{k}{1-\varepsilon^2}=\dfrac{2}{1-16/25}=\dfrac{2}{9/25}=\dfrac{50}{9}$, $b=\dfrac{k}{\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^2}}=\dfrac{2}{3/5}=\dfrac{10}{3}$, and the center is at $-a\varepsilon=-\dfrac{50}{9}\times\dfrac45=-\dfrac{40}{9}$; all of them agree.

Exercise 6.4: An alternative proof of the acceleration formula using the time derivatives of the basis vectors

Solution

Preparation: verifying $\dot{\bm e}_r=\dot\theta\bm e_\theta,\ \dot{\bm e}_\theta=-\dot\theta\bm e_r$. We differentiate the expression for the basis vector $\bm e_r=\cos\theta\bm e_x+\sin\theta\bm e_y$ ($\bm e_x,\bm e_y$ are constant vectors independent of time, and only $\theta=\theta(t)$ is a function of time) with respect to $t$. By the chain rule, $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}\cos\theta=-\dot\theta\sin\theta,\ \dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}\sin\theta=\dot\theta\cos\theta$, so

$$ \dot{\bm e}_r=-\dot\theta\sin\theta\,\bm e_x+\dot\theta\cos\theta\,\bm e_y=\dot\theta\left(-\sin\theta\,\bm e_x+\cos\theta\,\bm e_y\right)=\dot\theta\,\bm e_\theta $$

(the quantity in the last parentheses is exactly $\bm e_\theta$, by the equations of Chapter 6). Similarly, differentiating $\bm e_\theta=-\sin\theta\bm e_x+\cos\theta\bm e_y$ gives

$$ \dot{\bm e}_\theta=-\dot\theta\cos\theta\,\bm e_x-\dot\theta\sin\theta\,\bm e_y=-\dot\theta\left(\cos\theta\,\bm e_x+\sin\theta\,\bm e_y\right)=-\dot\theta\,\bm e_r $$

so both relations are verified.

Derivation of the velocity. The position vector can be written as $\bm r=r\bm e_r$ ($r,\bm e_r$ are both functions of time). Using the product rule for differentiation, we obtain

$$ \bm v=\dot{\bm r}=\frac{\dd}{\dd t}(r\bm e_r)=\dot r\,\bm e_r+r\,\dot{\bm e}_r=\dot r\,\bm e_r+r\left(\dot\theta\,\bm e_\theta\right)=\dot r\,\bm e_r+r\dot\theta\,\bm e_\theta $$

(we substituted $\dot{\bm e}_r=\dot\theta\bm e_\theta$). This is exactly the same result as Formula 6.1 of Chapter 6, $\bm v=\dot r\bm e_r+r\dot\theta\bm e_\theta$, so we have derived it without using the rotation matrix.

Derivation of the acceleration. We differentiate the velocity once more. This time we apply the product rule to each of the two terms of $\bm v=\dot r\bm e_r+r\dot\theta\bm e_\theta$. The first term is the product of $\dot r$ and $\bm e_r$, so

$$ \frac{\dd}{\dd t}\left(\dot r\,\bm e_r\right)=\ddot r\,\bm e_r+\dot r\,\dot{\bm e}_r=\ddot r\,\bm e_r+\dot r\dot\theta\,\bm e_\theta $$

The second term is a product of the three factors $r,\dot\theta,\bm e_\theta$, so we apply the product rule twice (the derivative of $fgh$ is $f'gh+fg'h+fgh'$). Letting $f=r,\ g=\dot\theta,\ h=\bm e_\theta$, we have $f'=\dot r,\ g'=\ddot\theta,\ h'=\dot{\bm e}_\theta=-\dot\theta\bm e_r$, so

$$ \frac{\dd}{\dd t}\left(r\dot\theta\,\bm e_\theta\right)=\dot r\dot\theta\,\bm e_\theta+r\ddot\theta\,\bm e_\theta+r\dot\theta\left(-\dot\theta\,\bm e_r\right)=-r\dot\theta^2\,\bm e_r+\left(\dot r\dot\theta+r\ddot\theta\right)\bm e_\theta $$

Adding the two results, we get

$$ \bm a=\dot{\bm v}=\left[\ddot r\,\bm e_r+\dot r\dot\theta\,\bm e_\theta\right]+\left[-r\dot\theta^2\,\bm e_r+\left(\dot r\dot\theta+r\ddot\theta\right)\bm e_\theta\right] $$

Collecting the coefficients of $\bm e_r$ and the coefficients of $\bm e_\theta$ separately, we have

$$ \bm a=\left(\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2\right)\bm e_r+\left(\dot r\dot\theta+\dot r\dot\theta+r\ddot\theta\right)\bm e_\theta=\left(\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2\right)\bm e_r+\left(2\dot r\dot\theta+r\ddot\theta\right)\bm e_\theta $$

which is exactly the same result as Formula 6.2 of Chapter 6. We have thus confirmed that two paths that look entirely different—the comparison of rotation matrices (the method of Chapter 6) and the time derivatives of the basis vectors (the method of this exercise)—lead to the same conclusion. This derivation also shows that the breakdown of $2\dot r\dot\theta$ is the sum of the $\dot r\dot\theta$ coming from the derivative of $\dot r\bm e_r$ and the $\dot r\dot\theta$ coming from the derivative of $r\dot\theta\bm e_\theta$.

Exercise 6.5: Checking the case of eccentricity 1 (parabola)

Solution

Substituting $\varepsilon=1$ into the general form $(1-\varepsilon^2)x^2+2k\varepsilon x+y^2-k^2=0$ in rectangular coordinates derived in Chapter 6, we have $(1-1^2)=0$, so the $x^2$ term vanishes exactly, leaving

$$ 2kx+y^2-k^2=0 $$

in which only a linear expression in $x$ remains. Arranging this into the form $y^2=\cdots$, we get

$$ y^2=k^2-2kx=-2k\left(x-\frac{k}{2}\right) $$

This is none other than the standard form $y^2=-2k\left(x-\dfrac{k}{2}\right)$ of a parabola with vertex $\left(\dfrac{k}{2},0\right)$ that opens in the negative $x$ direction (toward the origin). (It corresponds to taking the standard form $y^2=4px$ of High School Mathematics, Chapter 8, translating the vertex to $x=k/2$, and setting $4p=-2k$, that is, $p=-k/2$.) So we have indeed confirmed that the curve is a parabola. That the vertex lies at a distance $k/2$ from the focal point (the origin) is also consistent with the fact that, for $\varepsilon=1$, the perihelion distance is $r(0)=k/(1+1)=k/2$ by the result of Exercise 6.2.

Exercise 6.6: Numerical computation of the polar components of acceleration

Solution

Differentiating $r(t)=1+t^2$ gives $\dot r=2t\ [\mathrm{m/s}]$ and $\ddot r=2\ [\mathrm{m/s^2}]$. From $\theta(t)=t$ we have $\dot\theta=1\ \mathrm{rad/s}$ and $\ddot\theta=0$. Substituting $t=1\ \mathrm s$ gives

$$ r=2\ \mathrm m,\quad \dot r=2\ \mathrm{m/s},\quad \ddot r=2\ \mathrm{m/s^2},\quad \dot\theta=1\ \mathrm{rad/s},\quad \ddot\theta=0 $$

By Formula 6.2,

$$ a_r=\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2=2-2\times1^2=0\ \mathrm{m/s^2} $$ $$ a_\theta=2\dot r\dot\theta+r\ddot\theta=2\times2\times1+2\times0=4\ \mathrm{m/s^2} $$

Therefore the acceleration is only $4\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$ in the angular direction. Here $\ddot r=2\ \mathrm{m/s^2}\neq0=a_r$, so $\ddot r$ and $a_r$ are not equal—$\ddot r=2$ exactly cancels $-r\dot\theta^2=-2$, giving $a_r=0$. As a check, if we differentiate $x=(1+t^2)\cos t,\ y=(1+t^2)\sin t$ twice in rectangular coordinates, substitute $t=1$, and take the inner product (dot product) with $\bm e_r=(\cos1,\sin1),\ \bm e_\theta=(-\sin1,\cos1)$, we again obtain $a_r=0,\ a_\theta=4\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$.

Exercise 6.7: Polar components of uniform rectilinear motion

Solution

(1) From $r=\sqrt{b^2+u^2t^2}$ we have $r^2=b^2+u^2t^2$. Differentiating both sides with respect to $t$ gives $2r\dot r=2u^2t$, that is, $r\dot r=u^2t$, so $\dot r=\dfrac{u^2t}{r}=\dfrac{u^2t}{\sqrt{b^2+u^2t^2}}$. The angle is $\theta=\tan^{-1}\dfrac{ut}{b}$, and from $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}\tan^{-1}\dfrac{ut}{b}=\dfrac{1}{1+(ut/b)^2}\cdot\dfrac ub=\dfrac{bu}{b^2+u^2t^2}$ we obtain $\dot\theta=\dfrac{bu}{r^2}$.

(2) Differentiating $r\dot r=u^2t$ once more with respect to $t$ (product rule), we get $\dot r^2+r\ddot r=u^2$. Therefore

$$ \ddot r=\frac{u^2-\dot r^2}{r}=\frac{u^2}{r}\left(1-\frac{u^2t^2}{r^2}\right)=\frac{u^2}{r}\cdot\frac{r^2-u^2t^2}{r^2}=\frac{u^2b^2}{r^3} $$

(we used $r^2-u^2t^2=b^2$). On the other hand, $r\dot\theta^2=r\cdot\dfrac{b^2u^2}{r^4}=\dfrac{b^2u^2}{r^3}$, so $\ddot r=r\dot\theta^2$ holds. Hence $a_r=\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2=0$.

Next, differentiating $\dot\theta=bu\,r^{-2}$ gives $\ddot\theta=bu\cdot(-2)r^{-3}\dot r=-\dfrac{2bu\,\dot r}{r^3}$, so

$$ r\ddot\theta=-\frac{2bu\,\dot r}{r^2}=-2\dot r\cdot\frac{bu}{r^2}=-2\dot r\dot\theta $$

and thus $r\ddot\theta=-2\dot r\dot\theta$ holds. Hence $a_\theta=2\dot r\dot\theta+r\ddot\theta=0$.

This confirms, from the polar-coordinate formulas as well, that $a_r=a_\theta=0$ (that is, the acceleration of uniform rectilinear motion is $\bm 0$). However, since $\dot r\neq0,\ \dot\theta\neq0$ ($t\neq0$), the four terms $\ddot r$, $r\dot\theta^2$, $2\dot r\dot\theta$, and $r\ddot\theta$ are not individually zero; only their sums are zero. For example, for $b=u=1$ (in units of m and m/s) and $t=1\ \mathrm s$, we have $r=\sqrt2$, $\dot r=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt2}$, $\dot\theta=\dfrac12$, and $\ddot r=\dfrac{\sqrt2}{4}=r\dot\theta^2$, $2\dot r\dot\theta=\dfrac{\sqrt2}{2}=-r\ddot\theta$ (all in units of $\mathrm{m/s^2}$).

Exercise 6.8: Direction of the asymptotes of a hyperbola

Solution

In Formula 6.3, $r\to\infty$ occurs when the denominator $1+\varepsilon\cos\theta$ approaches $0$. The angle at which $\cos\theta=-\dfrac1\varepsilon$ (since $\varepsilon\gt1$, we have $-1\lt-\dfrac1\varepsilon\lt0$, so a solution exists) is $\theta_\infty$, and in the range $0\lt\theta_\infty\lt\pi$ we have

$$ \theta_\infty=\cos^{-1}\left(-\frac1\varepsilon\right)\qquad(\pi/2\lt\theta_\infty\lt\pi) $$

(the curve has $r\gt0$ in the range $-\theta_\infty\lt\theta\lt\theta_\infty$). Then $\sin\theta_\infty=\sqrt{1-\dfrac1{\varepsilon^2}}=\dfrac{\sqrt{\varepsilon^2-1}}{\varepsilon}$ (because $\sin\gt0$ for $0\lt\theta_\infty\lt\pi$), so

$$ \tan\theta_\infty=\frac{\sin\theta_\infty}{\cos\theta_\infty}=\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon^2-1}/\varepsilon}{-1/\varepsilon}=-\sqrt{\varepsilon^2-1} $$

For $\varepsilon=\dfrac53$, we have $\cos\theta_\infty=-\dfrac35$, $\sin\theta_\infty=\dfrac45$, and $\tan\theta_\infty=-\dfrac43$ ($\theta_\infty\approx126.9^\circ$).

By Theorem 6.1, the magnitude of the slope of the asymptotes of the hyperbola is $\dfrac ba=\dfrac{k/\sqrt{\varepsilon^2-1}}{k/(\varepsilon^2-1)}=\sqrt{\varepsilon^2-1}$, which for $\varepsilon=\dfrac53$ is $\sqrt{\dfrac{25}{9}-1}=\dfrac43$. Since $\left|\tan\theta_\infty\right|=\sqrt{\varepsilon^2-1}=\dfrac ba$, the direction $\theta_\infty$ in which the curve goes off to infinity coincides with the direction of the asymptotes of the hyperbola (whose slope with respect to the $x$-axis has magnitude $b/a$).

← Back to Chapter 6, “Motion in Polar Coordinates and Conic Sections”

Chapter 7 Simple Harmonic, Damped, and Forced Oscillations

Exercise 7.1: Discriminant and types of damping

Solution

Solving the characteristic equation $m\lambda^2+n\lambda+k=0$ of the equation of motion $m\ddot x+n\dot x+kx=0$ ($m,n,k\gt 0$) with the quadratic formula, we obtain

$$ \lambda=\frac{-n\pm\sqrt{n^2-4mk}}{2m}=\frac{-n\pm\sqrt{D}}{2m},\qquad D=n^2-4mk $$

The sign of the radicand $D$ determines whether the roots are real or complex—this is the reason the sign of $D$ corresponds to the type of damping.

  • When $D\lt 0$: Since the radicand is negative, $\sqrt{D}=i\sqrt{-D}$ ($i$ is the imaginary unit), and the two roots become the mutually conjugate complex numbers $\lambda=\alpha\pm i\omega_d$ ($\alpha=-n/(2m)$, $\omega_d=\sqrt{-D}/(2m)$). Having an imaginary part $\omega_d\neq0$ means that oscillating terms of the form $\cos\omega_d t,\sin\omega_d t$ appear in the general solution, and since the real part satisfies $\alpha\lt 0$, the amplitude decays exponentially. Decaying while oscillating—this is damped oscillation.
  • When $D\gt 0$: Since the radicand is positive, $\sqrt{D}$ is real, and the two roots $\lambda_1,\lambda_2$ are both real. Moreover, since $m,n,k\gt 0$ gives $\sqrt{D}=\sqrt{n^2-4mk}\lt n$, both $\lambda_{1,2}=(-n\pm\sqrt{D})/(2m)$ are negative. The general solution $x=C_1e^{\lambda_1t}+C_2e^{\lambda_2t}$ is a sum of two real exponential functions and contains no trigonometric functions, so it does not oscillate. Since an exponential function with a negative real exponent approaches 0 monotonically in both cases, $x(t)$ also approaches 0 monotonically, without oscillating—this is overdamping.
  • When $D=0$: Since the radicand is 0, the two roots coincide in the single value $\lambda_0=-n/(2m)$ (a double root). In this case the form of the general solution for two real roots cannot be used as it stands (because only one linearly independent fundamental solution can be built), and we must use $t\,e^{\lambda_0t}$ as the other fundamental solution. The general solution $x=(C_1+C_2t)e^{\lambda_0t}$ also does not oscillate, but because it lies exactly on the boundary between the case $D\gt 0$ and the case $D\lt 0$ (the boundary where the discriminant is 0), it is called critical damping.

To summarize, the sign of $D=n^2-4mk$ determines whether the characteristic roots are real or complex: if they are complex, the motion decays while oscillating ($D\lt 0$, damped oscillation); if they are real, it decays without oscillating ($D\gt 0$, overdamping); and the boundary where the roots just become a double root is critical damping ($D=0$).

Exercise 7.2: Maximum velocity and maximum acceleration in simple harmonic motion

Solution

The energy conservation law for simple harmonic motion is $\frac12m\dot x^2+\frac12kx^2=C$ (constant), and substituting $x=A,\dot x=0$ (the instant of momentary rest at the position of the amplitude) gives $C=\frac12kA^2$ (Law 7.1 in the main text). Therefore, at any time,

$$ \frac12m\dot x^2+\frac12kx^2=\frac12kA^2 $$

holds. Since the right-hand side is constant, the magnitude of the velocity $|\dot x|$ is largest when the second term $\frac12kx^2$ on the left-hand side is smallest ($x=0$). Substituting $x=0$, we obtain

$$ \frac12m\dot x_{\max}^2=\frac12kA^2 \qquad\therefore\ \dot x_{\max}=\sqrt{\frac{k}{m}}\,A=\omega A $$

($\omega=\sqrt{k/m}$ is the angular frequency). This agrees exactly with the maximum value $|\dot x|_{\max}=A\omega$ (attained when $|\sin\omega t|=1$) of $\dot x(t)=-A\omega\sin\omega t$, which is obtained by differentiating $x(t)=A\cos\omega t$.
