For the maximum acceleration, we use the equation of motion $m\ddot x=-kx$, which gives the relation $\ddot x=-(k/m)x=-\omega^2x$ (proportional to the position and opposite in direction). Since $|x|$ is largest at $x=\pm A$ (the positions of the amplitude),

$$ |\ddot x|_{\max}=\omega^2A $$

holds. To summarize, the maximum velocity is $\omega A$ (at the instant of passing through $x=0$), and the maximum acceleration is $\omega^2A$ (at the instant of reaching $x=\pm A$).

Exercise 7.3: The drag coefficient for critical damping

Solution

Critical damping is the case in which the discriminant of the characteristic equation is 0 (Chapter 7, Theorem 7.2 (iii)):

$$ D=n^2-4mk=0 \qquad\therefore\ n^2=4mk $$

Since $n$ is the drag coefficient and must physically satisfy $n\gt 0$, we choose the positive square root:

$$ n=\sqrt{4mk}=2\sqrt{mk} $$

This is the drag coefficient that makes a system with mass $m$ and spring constant $k$ critically damped. For example, for the same system as in Example 7.5 of Chapter 7, with $m=1\ \mathrm{kg},k=5\ \mathrm{N/m}$, we have $n=2\sqrt{1\times5}=2\sqrt5\approx4.47\ \mathrm{N\cdot s/m}$; if $n$ is smaller than this, the system is overdamped ($D\gt 0$), and if it is larger, the system undergoes damped oscillation ($D\lt 0$).

Exercise 7.4: The amplitude of a forced oscillation and its limit

Solution

The steady-state response of the forced oscillation found in Chapter 7 has the form $x_0(t)=X_0\cos(\omega t-\delta)$, and its amplitude is

$$ X_0=\frac{f_0}{\sqrt{(k-m\omega^2)^2+(n\omega)^2}} $$

Consider the limit $\omega\to0$. In the denominator, $(k-m\omega^2)^2$ becomes $(k-0)^2=k^2$ at $\omega=0$, and $(n\omega)^2$ becomes $0$ at $\omega=0$. Therefore,

$$ \lim_{\omega\to0}X_0=\frac{f_0}{\sqrt{k^2+0}}=\frac{f_0}{\sqrt{k^2}}=\frac{f_0}{k} $$

(since $k\gt 0$, $\sqrt{k^2}=k$). This agrees with the intuition that a driving force of very small angular frequency is close to “a constant force $f_0$ that hardly varies with time,” and the mass stays (almost statically) near the position $x_{\text{st}}=f_0/k$ at which the spring force balances the constant force, as given by Hooke's law of the spring, $f_0=kx_{\text{st}}$.

Exercise 7.5: Correspondence between notations of the operator method

Solution

The correspondence between the operator method of University Mathematics, Chapter 38, and the operator method used in the “Math Tool” of Chapter 7 is organized in the table below.

ItemAD 02, Chapter 38Chapter 7 (this chapter)
Independent variable$x$ (a mathematical variable)$t$ (time)
Definition of the differential operator$D\equiv\dfrac{\dd}{\dd x}$ (Section 38.1)$D\equiv\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}$ (the same symbol carried over unchanged)
Notation for operators up to second order$Dy=y',\ D^2y=y''$$Dx=\dot x,\ D^2x=\ddot x$
Polynomial operator$\Phi(D)=D^2+aD+b$ (leading coefficient 1, from Section 38.3 onward)$\Phi(D)=mD^2+nD+k$ (leading coefficient $m$; the form used as it stands, without dividing both sides of the equation by $m$)
Substitution formula for exponential functions$\Phi(D)y=e^{\alpha x}$ has the particular solution $y=\dfrac{e^{\alpha x}}{\Phi(\alpha)}$ ($\Phi(\alpha)\neq0$, Section 38.4)$\Phi(D)x=f_0e^{i\omega t}$ has the particular solution $x=\dfrac{f_0e^{i\omega t}}{\Phi(i\omega)}$ ($\Phi(i\omega)\neq0$)
Value substituted in the exponentAn arbitrary real (or complex) number $\alpha$A purely imaginary number $\alpha=i\omega$ (to treat a trigonometric external force as a complex exponential function)

There is only one essential difference: in AD 02, Chapter 38, the leading coefficient is normalized to 1 and the operator is handled in the form $\Phi(D)=D^2+aD+b$, whereas in Chapter 7 both sides of the equation of motion are not divided by the mass $m$, and $\Phi(D)=mD^2+nD+k$ (leading coefficient $m$) is used as it stands. The formula $x=e^{\alpha t}/\Phi(\alpha)$ itself holds regardless of the leading coefficient of $\Phi(D)$ (as we checked in Example 7.6, as long as $\Phi(\alpha)\ne0$, substituting $x=e^{\alpha t}/\Phi(\alpha)$ directly confirms that it satisfies $\Phi(D)x=e^{\alpha t}$), so it can be used in the same way with either normalization.

Exercise 7.6: Derivation of the resonance angular frequency (Advanced)

Solution

Maximizing the amplitude $X_0(\omega)=f_0/\sqrt{(k-m\omega^2)^2+(n\omega)^2}$ is the same as minimizing the quantity under the square root in the denominator,

$$ D(\omega)\equiv(k-m\omega^2)^2+(n\omega)^2 $$

(since the numerator $f_0$ is a positive constant independent of $\omega$, and the square root and its radicand increase and decrease in the same direction). We differentiate $D(\omega)$ with respect to $\omega$. First, differentiating the first term by the chain rule for composite functions, we obtain

$$ \frac{\dd}{\dd\omega}(k-m\omega^2)^2=2(k-m\omega^2)\cdot\frac{\dd}{\dd\omega}(k-m\omega^2)=2(k-m\omega^2)\cdot(-2m\omega)=-4m\omega(k-m\omega^2) $$

The second term gives

$$ \frac{\dd}{\dd\omega}(n\omega)^2=\frac{\dd}{\dd\omega}(n^2\omega^2)=2n^2\omega $$

and hence

$$ D'(\omega)=-4m\omega(k-m\omega^2)+2n^2\omega=2\omega\left[-2m(k-m\omega^2)+n^2\right]=2\omega\left(2m^2\omega^2-2mk+n^2\right) $$

$D'(\omega)=0$ holds either at $\omega=0$ (this is a local maximum point, and since $D(0)=k^2\gt 0$ it is not the minimum—indeed, as we saw in Section 7.4, $X_0\to f_0/k$, a finite value, as $\omega\to0$, and as $\omega$ is increased the amplitude becomes still larger near resonance, so $\omega=0$ is not the minimum point), or when

$$ 2m^2\omega^2-2mk+n^2=0 \qquad\therefore\ \omega^2=\frac{2mk-n^2}{2m^2}=\frac{k}{m}-\frac{n^2}{2m^2}=\omega_0^2-\frac{n^2}{2m^2} $$

(where $\omega_0=\sqrt{k/m}$). Only when this right-hand side is positive ($\omega_0^2\gt n^2/(2m^2)$, that is, when the resistance is weak) does a real solution with $\omega\neq0$,

$$ \omega_{\text{res}}=\sqrt{\omega_0^2-\frac{n^2}{2m^2}} $$

exist. This is the resonance angular frequency given by the equation in the main text (Eq. (7.20) of Chapter 7). (That this $\omega$ is actually the minimum point of $D(\omega)$, that is, the maximum point of $X_0(\omega)$, can be confirmed from the fact that $D(\omega)$ takes large values both as $\omega\to0$ and as $\omega\to\infty$, and that the only stationary point in between is this $\omega_{\text{res}}$.)

← Back to Chapter 7, “Simple Harmonic, Damped, and Forced Oscillations”

Chapter 8 Central Forces and Planetary Motion — Kepler's Laws and the Earth Tunnel

Exercise 8.1: Constant areal velocity and conservation of angular momentum

Solution

Equation ② that appeared in the derivation in Section 8.2 was $\dfrac1r\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}(r^2\dot\theta)=0$. Multiplying both sides by $r$ ($\ne0$) gives $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}(r^2\dot\theta)=0$, which means that $r^2\dot\theta$ takes a constant value independent of the time $t$. We therefore let this constant value be $C_1$:

$$ r^2\dot\theta=C_1=\text{constant} $$

On the other hand, when the position vector of the particle (point mass) rotates through an angle $\dd\theta$ between time $t$ and $t+\dd t$, the infinitesimal area it sweeps out can be written, as the area of a circular sector of radius $r$ and central angle $\dd\theta$, as $\dd A=\dfrac12r^2\,\dd\theta$. Dividing both sides by $\dd t$, the areal velocity is

$$ \frac{\dd A}{\dd t}=\frac12r^2\frac{\dd\theta}{\dd t}=\frac12r^2\dot\theta=\frac12(r^2\dot\theta)=\frac{C_1}{2} $$

Since $C_1$ is a constant independent of time, the areal velocity $\dd A/\dd t=C_1/2$ is also constant in time. In other words, the content of Eq. ②, “$r^2\dot\theta$ is constant,” states exactly the same thing as Kepler's second law (the law of constant areal velocity), “the line segment joining the particle and the center sweeps out equal areas in equal times”—for the areal velocity itself is nothing but the quantity $r^2\dot\theta$ multiplied by $\dfrac12$. This result also agrees with $L=mr^2\dot\theta=$ constant, which we derived from conservation of angular momentum in Section 8.1, and the two constants are related by $C_1=L/m$.

Exercise 8.2: Finding the mass of the Sun from Jupiter's data

Solution

Solving Kepler's third law (Chapter 8, Law 8.4) $T^2=\dfrac{4\pi^2}{GM}a^3$ for $GM$ gives

$$ GM=\frac{4\pi^2a^3}{T^2} $$

Substituting Jupiter's data $a=5.203\ \mathrm{AU}=5.203\times1.496\times10^{11}\ \mathrm{m}=7.784\times10^{11}\ \mathrm{m}$ and $T=11.86\ \mathrm{yr}=11.86\times3.156\times10^{7}\ \mathrm{s}=3.743\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{s}$, we obtain

$$ GM_\odot=\frac{4\pi^2\times(7.784\times10^{11}\ \mathrm{m})^3}{(3.743\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{s})^2} =\frac{4\pi^2\times4.716\times10^{35}\ \mathrm{m^3}}{1.401\times10^{17}\ \mathrm{s^2}} \approx1.329\times10^{20}\ \mathrm{m^3/s^2} $$

The value obtained from the Earth's data in Example 8.3(2) of Chapter 8 was $GM_\odot\approx1.3270\times10^{20}\ \mathrm{m^3/s^2}$ (which agreed well with the literature value), and its relative difference from the value obtained from Jupiter's data, $1.329\times10^{20}\ \mathrm{m^3/s^2}$ (more precisely, $1.3288\times10^{20}$), is about $0.14\ \%$. This difference is due not only to rounding error but also to a deviation from the approximation that “the Sun is at rest” (strictly, $M$ in Law 8.4 must be replaced by $M+m$), since Jupiter's mass is about $1/1000$ that of the Sun. From the data of the Earth, an inner planet, and Jupiter, an outer planet—data with completely different semi-major axes and periods—the same solar mass (multiplied by $G$) is obtained with good agreement. This is a confirmation, by observational data, that the proportionality constant $4\pi^2/(GM)$ of Kepler's third law does not change from planet to planet (it is determined only by the central body).

Exercise 8.3: Proof that the period of a chord-shaped tunnel is unchanged (Advanced)

Solution

We express the position vector of the particle, using the unit vector $\bm e$ along the tunnel and the unit vector $\bm e_\perp$ perpendicular to it, as $\bm r=p\,\bm e_\perp+s\,\bm e$ ($p$ is the length of the perpendicular dropped from the center $\mathrm O$ to the tunnel, which is constant, and $s$ is the coordinate along the tunnel). Extending Law 8.6 of Chapter 8 to the form of a three-dimensional vector, the gravitational force inside a uniform sphere ($|\bm r|\lt R$) on a particle of mass $m$ is

$$ \bm F(\bm r)=-\frac43\pi G\rho\,m\,\bm r $$

(its magnitude is proportional to the distance $|\bm r|$ from the center, and its direction always points toward the center $\mathrm O$). The component of this force in the tunnel direction (the $\bm e$ direction), obtained by substituting $\bm r=p\,\bm e_\perp+s\,\bm e$, is

$$ \bm F\cdot\bm e=-\frac43\pi G\rho\,m\,\bm r\cdot\bm e =-\frac43\pi G\rho\,m\,\big(p\,\bm e_\perp\cdot\bm e+s\,\bm e\cdot\bm e\big) $$

Since $\bm e_\perp$ and $\bm e$ are, by definition, the direction perpendicular to the tunnel and the direction along the tunnel, they are orthogonal to each other, and $\bm e_\perp\cdot\bm e=0$ (by the definition of the inner product, or dot product, the inner product of two orthogonal vectors is 0). Also, since $\bm e$ is a unit vector, $\bm e\cdot\bm e=|\bm e|^2=1$. Therefore the term containing $p$ vanishes, and

$$ \bm F\cdot\bm e=-\frac43\pi G\rho\,m\,s $$

This shows that the component of the force in the tunnel direction does not depend at all on the perpendicular length $p$ (how far the tunnel is from the center); it is determined only by the coordinate $s$ along the tunnel. The tunnel wall is smooth and frictionless, and the normal force that the wall exerts on the particle cancels only the component in the $\bm e_\perp$ direction (it exerts no force along the tunnel), so for the equation of motion in the $\bm e$ direction we need only consider the $\bm e$ component of the force:

$$ m\ddot s=\bm F\cdot\bm e=-\frac43\pi G\rho\,m\,s \qquad\therefore\ \ddot s+\omega^2s=0,\qquad \omega=\sqrt{\frac43\pi G\rho} $$

This is an equation of simple harmonic motion with exactly the same angular frequency $\omega$ as the equation of motion (Section 8.5.3) of the tunnel through the center in Figure 8.8 (the special case $p=0$). Therefore the period of oscillation in the chord-shaped tunnel, $T=2\pi/\omega$, does not depend at all on how far the tunnel is from the center (the value of $p$, that is, the length of the tunnel), and is the same as for the tunnel through the center, about 84 minutes.

Exercise 8.4: The Earth tunnel and conservation of energy

Solution

From the force $F(r)=-\dfrac43\pi G\rho\,mr$ of Law 8.6 of Chapter 8, we find the potential energy $U(r)$ (with $U(0)=0$ as the reference) by the inverse operation (integration) of $F=-\dd U/\dd r$:

$$ U(r)=-\int_0^rF(r')\,\dd r'=-\int_0^r\left(-\frac43\pi G\rho\,mr'\right)\dd r' =\frac43\pi G\rho\,m\int_0^rr'\,\dd r' =\frac43\pi G\rho\,m\left[\frac{r'^2}{2}\right]_0^r =\frac23\pi G\rho\,m\,r^2 $$

The mechanical energy is $E=\frac12m\dot r^2+U(r)=\frac12m\dot r^2+\frac23\pi G\rho\,mr^2$. Substituting the initial conditions $r(0)=R,\ \dot r(0)=0$, we obtain

$$ E=\frac12m\times0^2+\frac23\pi G\rho\,mR^2=\frac23\pi G\rho\,mR^2 $$

Next, differentiating the solution $r(t)=R\cos\omega t$ ($\omega=\sqrt{\frac43\pi G\rho}$) of the equation of motion gives $\dot r(t)=-R\omega\sin\omega t$, so

$$ \dot r^2=R^2\omega^2\sin^2\omega t=R^2\omega^2\big(1-\cos^2\omega t\big)=\omega^2\big(R^2-R^2\cos^2\omega t\big)=\omega^2(R^2-r^2) $$

(we used $\sin^2\omega t=1-\cos^2\omega t$ and $R\cos\omega t=r(t)$). Substituting this into the expression for the energy gives

$$ \frac12m\dot r^2+U(r)=\frac12m\,\omega^2(R^2-r^2)+\frac23\pi G\rho\,mr^2 $$

Substituting $\omega^2=\frac43\pi G\rho$ and simplifying, we get

$$ =\frac12m\cdot\frac43\pi G\rho\,(R^2-r^2)+\frac23\pi G\rho\,mr^2 =\frac23\pi G\rho\,m\,R^2-\frac23\pi G\rho\,mr^2+\frac23\pi G\rho\,mr^2 =\frac23\pi G\rho\,mR^2 $$

The terms $-\frac23\pi G\rho\,mr^2$ and $+\frac23\pi G\rho\,mr^2$ cancel each other, leaving the constant value $\frac23\pi G\rho\,mR^2$, which does not depend on $r$. This agrees exactly with $E=\frac23\pi G\rho\,mR^2$, which we found earlier from the initial conditions. Therefore the solution $r(t)=R\cos\omega t$ satisfies the law of conservation of energy $\frac12m\dot r^2+U(r)=E$ (constant) at every instant, and we have confirmed that it is consistent as a solution of the equation of motion.

Exercise 8.5: Escape velocity of Mars

Solution

Substituting the data for Mars, $GM_{\text{Mars}}=4.283\times10^{13}\ \mathrm{m^3/s^2}$ and $R_{\text{Mars}}=3.390\times10^6\ \mathrm{m}$, into the escape velocity formula $v_{\rm esc}=\sqrt{2GM/R}$ of Chapter 8, we obtain

$$ v_{\rm esc,\text{Mars}}=\sqrt{\frac{2\times4.283\times10^{13}\ \mathrm{m^3/s^2}}{3.390\times10^6\ \mathrm{m}}} =\sqrt{2.527\times10^{7}\ \mathrm{m^2/s^2}} \approx5.03\times10^3\ \mathrm{m/s}\approx5.03\ \mathrm{km/s} $$

Comparing this with the escape velocity of the Earth, $\approx11.2\ \mathrm{km/s}$, and that of the Moon, $\approx2.38\ \mathrm{km/s}$, found in Example 8.6 of Chapter 8, we see that the escape velocity of Mars lies between them (about 45% of the Earth's and about 2.1 times the Moon's). This reflects the fact that both the mass and the radius of Mars are smaller than the Earth's but larger than the Moon's ($GM_{\text{Mars}}/GM_\oplus\approx0.107$, $R_{\text{Mars}}/R_\oplus\approx0.532$). It is because of this difference in escape velocity that an actual Mars probe (for example, in a Mars sample-return mission) needs a smaller velocity to take off from Mars than is needed for a launch from the Earth.

Exercise 8.6: Orbital radius of a geostationary satellite

Solution

A geostationary satellite revolves around the Earth in a circular orbit (eccentricity $\varepsilon=0$, semi-major axis $a=r_0$) with the same period as the Earth's rotation period, $T_{\rm sid}=86164\ \mathrm{s}$. Solving Kepler's third law (Chapter 8, Law 8.4) $T^2=\dfrac{4\pi^2}{GM}a^3$ for $a^3$ gives

$$ a^3=\frac{GM\,T^2}{4\pi^2} $$

Substituting the Earth's $GM_\oplus=3.986\times10^{14}\ \mathrm{m^3/s^2}$ and $T_{\rm sid}=86164\ \mathrm{s}$, we obtain

$$ r_0^3=a^3=\frac{3.986\times10^{14}\ \mathrm{m^3/s^2}\times(86164\ \mathrm{s})^2}{4\pi^2} \approx\frac{3.986\times10^{14}\times7.424\times10^9}{39.48}\ \mathrm{m^3} \approx7.497\times10^{22}\ \mathrm{m^3} $$

Taking the cube root,

$$ r_0\approx4.216\times10^7\ \mathrm{m}=42164\ \mathrm{km} $$

(Converting to the altitude above the Earth's surface gives $42164-6371\approx35793\ \mathrm{km}$, which agrees with the well-known altitude of a geostationary satellite, “about 36,000 km.”) Next, using the circular-orbit speed $v_{\rm circ}=\sqrt{GM/r_0}$ (Section 8.4; from the balance of the centripetal force, $GMm/r^2=mv^2/r$), the angular momentum per unit mass can be written as

$$ \frac{L}{m}=r_0v_{\rm circ}=r_0\sqrt{\frac{GM}{r_0}}=\sqrt{GM\,r_0} $$

Substituting numerical values,

$$ \frac{L}{m}=\sqrt{3.986\times10^{14}\ \mathrm{m^3/s^2}\times4.216\times10^{7}\ \mathrm{m}}\approx1.296\times10^{11}\ \mathrm{m^2/s} $$

That this satisfies $r_0=L^2/(GMm^2)=(L/m)^2/(GM)$ of Chapter 8 can be confirmed from $(1.296\times10^{11})^2/(3.986\times10^{14})\approx4.216\times10^{7}\ \mathrm{m}$ (it agrees with the value of $r_0$).

Exercise 8.7: Reproducing the substitution $u=1/r$ from scratch

Solution

Since $r$ is a function $r(\theta)$ of $\theta$, and $\theta$ is a function $\theta(t)$ of $t$, the chain rule gives $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}=\dot\theta\,\dfrac{\dd}{\dd\theta}$. Using $\dot\theta=C_1/r^2$ (from $r^2\dot\theta=C_1$), we obtain

$$ \dot r=\frac{\dd r}{\dd\theta}\,\dot\theta=\frac{C_1}{r^2}\frac{\dd r}{\dd\theta} $$

Letting $u=1/r$, we have $\dfrac{\dd u}{\dd\theta}=\dfrac{\dd u}{\dd r}\dfrac{\dd r}{\dd\theta}=-\dfrac1{r^2}\dfrac{\dd r}{\dd\theta}$, so $\dfrac1{r^2}\dfrac{\dd r}{\dd\theta}=-\dfrac{\dd u}{\dd\theta}$, and therefore

$$ \dot r=-C_1\frac{\dd u}{\dd\theta} $$

Next, we substitute the above $\dot r$ into $\ddot r=\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}\dot r=\dot\theta\dfrac{\dd}{\dd\theta}\dot r$ ($C_1$ is a constant):

$$ \ddot r=\frac{C_1}{r^2}\frac{\dd}{\dd\theta}\left(-C_1\frac{\dd u}{\dd\theta}\right)=-\frac{C_1^2}{r^2}\frac{\dd^2u}{\dd\theta^2} $$

We substitute these into $\ddot r\,r^2-C_1^2/r=-GM$. Noting that $C_1^2/r=C_1^2u$, we have

$$ r^2\left(-\frac{C_1^2}{r^2}\frac{\dd^2u}{\dd\theta^2}\right)-C_1^2u=-GM \quad\Longrightarrow\quad -C_1^2\frac{\dd^2u}{\dd\theta^2}-C_1^2u=-GM $$

Dividing both sides by $-C_1^2\ (\ne0)$, we obtain $\dfrac{\dd^2u}{\dd\theta^2}+u=\dfrac{GM}{C_1^2}$.

Exercise 8.8: Correspondence table between the Earth tunnel and the spring–mass system

Solution

The Earth tunnel obeys $m\ddot r=-\dfrac43\pi G\rho\,m\,r$, and the spring–mass oscillator obeys $m\ddot x=-k_{\rm s}x$. Comparing the coefficients, under the correspondence $x\leftrightarrow r$ we have $k_{\rm s}\leftrightarrow\dfrac43\pi G\rho\,m$. Accordingly, the angular frequency and the period correspond as follows.

QuantitySpring–mass oscillatorEarth tunnel
Position$x$$r$ (signed distance from the center)
Restoring force$-k_{\rm s}x$$-\frac43\pi G\rho\,m\,r$
Spring constant$k_{\rm s}$$\frac43\pi G\rho\,m$
Angular frequency$\omega=\sqrt{k_{\rm s}/m}$$\omega=\sqrt{\frac43\pi G\rho}$
Period$T=2\pi\sqrt{m/k_{\rm s}}$$T=2\pi\sqrt{3/(4\pi G\rho)}$

The numerical values, for $m=1\ \mathrm{kg}$ and $\rho=5500\ \mathrm{kg/m^3}$, are

$$ k_{\rm s}=\frac43\pi G\rho\,m=\frac43\pi\times6.674\times10^{-11}\ \mathrm{N\,m^2/kg^2}\times5500\ \mathrm{kg/m^3}\times1\ \mathrm{kg}\approx1.537\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{N/m} $$

(The unit is $\mathrm{N\,m^2/kg^2\cdot kg/m^3\cdot kg}=\mathrm{N/m}$, which agrees with the unit of the spring constant.) This is an extremely soft spring (stretching it by $1\ \mathrm m$ gives a force of only $1.5\ \mathrm{\mu N}$), but because the distance the particle moves is very large, the Earth's radius $6.4\times10^6\ \mathrm m$, the maximum force becomes $k_{\rm s}R\approx9.8\ \mathrm N$ (which is just the gravitational force acting on a particle of $1\ \mathrm{kg}$). Note also that here $\omega=\sqrt{k_{\rm s}/m}=\sqrt{1.537\times10^{-6}}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}\approx1.24\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, which agrees with the value in the main text.

Exercise 8.9: Length and transit time of a chord-shaped tunnel

Solution

(1) Let $s_{\max}$ be the distance from the midpoint of the tunnel (the foot of the perpendicular) to an endpoint. Since the endpoint lies on the surface of the sphere, the Pythagorean theorem for the right triangle formed by the foot of the perpendicular, the center, and the endpoint gives $s_{\max}^2+p^2=R^2$.

$$ s_{\max}=\sqrt{R^2-p^2}=\sqrt{(6371\ \mathrm{km})^2-(3000\ \mathrm{km})^2}=\sqrt{3.1590\times10^{7}}\ \mathrm{km}\approx5620\ \mathrm{km} $$

The length of the tunnel is $2s_{\max}\approx1.124\times10^4\ \mathrm{km}$.

(2) By the result of Exercise 8.3, the motion along the tunnel obeys $\ddot s=-\omega^2s$ ($\omega=\sqrt{\frac43\pi G\rho}=1.240\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$), and if the particle is released from rest at one end $s=-s_{\max}$, then $s(t)=-s_{\max}\cos\omega t$. It reaches the opposite end $s=+s_{\max}$ when $\omega t=\pi$, which is half a period:

$$ t=\frac T2=\frac{\pi}{\omega}=\frac{\pi}{1.240\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}}\approx2.53\times10^{3}\ \mathrm s\approx42.2\ \mathrm{min} $$

This is exactly the same as the one-way time ($T/2$) of the tunnel through the center. The tunnel is much shorter, but the force (the right-hand side of the equation of motion) is correspondingly smaller, so the time does not change.

(3) The speed is greatest at the midpoint $s=0$, where $v_{\max}=\omega s_{\max}$:

$$ v_{\max}=1.240\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}\times5.620\times10^{6}\ \mathrm m\approx6.97\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{m/s}\approx7.0\ \mathrm{km/s} $$

(This is smaller than the $7.9\ \mathrm{km/s}$ for the tunnel through the center, because the amplitude is only $s_{\max}$.)

← Back to Chapter 8, “Central Forces and Planetary Motion — Kepler's Laws and the Earth Tunnel”

Chapter 9 Moving Reference Frames and Fictitious Forces — Centrifugal and Coriolis Forces

Exercise 9.1: A hanging strap in a train braking suddenly

Solution

Take the direction of travel of the train as forward. The train moves forward while decelerating with magnitude $a$, so the acceleration vector of the train (the reference frame) points opposite to the direction of travel (backward) and has magnitude $a$. Working in the non-inertial frame fixed to the train, three forces act on the strap (mass $m$): gravity $mg$ (downward), the tension $T$ of the cord (along the cord), and the fictitious force (inertial force), which points opposite to the acceleration of the frame, that is, forward, with magnitude $ma$. The strap is at rest, in equilibrium.

Suppose the cord is tilted forward from the vertical by an angle $\alpha$. From the balance in the vertical direction, $T\cos\alpha=mg$, and from the balance in the horizontal direction, $T\sin\alpha=ma$ (since the fictitious force pushes the strap forward, the cord tilts forward from the vertical). Taking the ratio of the two equations,

$$ \tan\alpha=\frac{T\sin\alpha}{T\cos\alpha}=\frac{ma}{mg}=\frac{a}{g} $$

Substituting the numerical values,

$$ \tan\alpha=\frac{3.0}{9.8}\approx0.3061 \qquad\therefore\ \alpha=\arctan(0.3061)\approx17.0^\circ $$

The strap comes to equilibrium tilted from the vertical by about $17^\circ$ in the direction of travel of the train (this is the same reasoning as why passengers' bodies lurch forward under sudden braking). $\tan\alpha$ does not depend on $m$.

Exercise 9.2: The water surface in a rotating bucket

Solution

(1) In the frame that rotates with the bucket with angular velocity $\omega$, the water is at rest. A small element of water at the surface (mass $\Delta m$) is acted on by gravity $\Delta m\,g$ (downward), the centrifugal force $\Delta m\,\omega^2\rho$ (outward from the axis of rotation, horizontal), and a force due to the pressure, directed perpendicular to the water surface. Since the pressure force is perpendicular to the surface, the force balance along the surface means that the component along the surface of the resultant of gravity and the centrifugal force is zero, that is, that the resultant is perpendicular to the water surface.

Consider the cross section described by the distance $\rho$ from the axis of rotation and the vertically upward coordinate $z$. The resultant force (per unit mass) is $(\omega^2\rho,\ -g)$. The tangent direction of the water surface $z(\rho)$ is $(1,\ \dd z/\dd\rho)$, so the condition that this be perpendicular to the resultant force (inner product, or dot product, equal to zero) is

$$ \omega^2\rho\times1+(-g)\times\diff{z}{\rho}=0 \qquad\therefore\quad \diff{z}{\rho}=\frac{\omega^2\rho}{g} $$

Integrating this with $z=z_0$ at $\rho=0$ gives

$$ z(\rho)=z_0+\frac{\omega^2}{2g}\rho^2 $$

so the water surface is a paraboloid of revolution.

(2) $60\ \mathrm{rpm}$ is one revolution per second, so $\omega=2\pi\ \mathrm{rad/s}\approx6.283\ \mathrm{rad/s}$. The difference in height between the rim $\rho=a=0.15\ \mathrm m$ and the center is

$$ z(a)-z_0=\frac{\omega^2a^2}{2g}=\frac{(6.283)^2\times(0.15)^2}{2\times9.8}=\frac{39.48\times0.0225}{19.6}\approx0.0453\ \mathrm m $$

that is, about $4.5\ \mathrm{cm}$ (the units are $\mathrm{s^{-2}\cdot m^2/(m/s^2)}=\mathrm m$). The faster the rotation ($\propto\omega^2$), the higher the water is at the rim and the lower it is at the center.

Exercise 9.3: A bead sliding along a rotating rod

Solution

(1) Take $\bm e_r'$ outward along the rod and $\bm e_\theta'$ in the counterclockwise direction perpendicular to it in the horizontal plane. Here $\bm\omega=\omega\bm e_z$ ($\omega=2.0\ \mathrm{rad/s}$, vertically upward). Since the bead moves only along the rod, $\bm v_{\text{rel}}=\dot r\,\bm e_r'$. The forces acting on the bead (in the rotating frame) are as follows.

  • Centrifugal force: $m\omega^2r\,\bm e_r'$ (outward along the rod).
  • Coriolis force: $-2m\bm\omega\times\bm v_{\text{rel}}=-2m\omega\dot r\,(\bm e_z\times\bm e_r')=-2m\omega\dot r\,\bm e_\theta'$ (perpendicular to the rod, to the right of the direction of motion).
  • Gravity, and the vertical normal force with which the rod supports the bead (these balance). Since the rod is smooth, the horizontal force exerted by the rod is perpendicular to the rod and can be written $N\bm e_\theta'$.

The equation of motion along the rod is

$$ m\ddot r=m\omega^2r\qquad\therefore\quad\ddot r=\omega^2r $$

The general solution is $r=Ce^{\omega t}+De^{-\omega t}$. From the initial conditions $r(0)=r_0$ and $\dot r(0)=0$ we get $C+D=r_0$ and $\omega(C-D)=0$, so $C=D=r_0/2$, and therefore

$$ r(t)=\frac{r_0}{2}\left(e^{\omega t}+e^{-\omega t}\right)=r_0\cosh\omega t $$

The bead is accelerated outward exponentially.

(2) Setting $r=1.0\ \mathrm m$, we have $\cosh\omega t=1.0/0.10=10$, so $\omega t=\cosh^{-1}10=\ln(10+\sqrt{99})\approx2.993$. Therefore

$$ t=\frac{2.993}{2.0}\approx1.5\ \mathrm s $$

(3) In the direction perpendicular to the rod (the $\bm e_\theta'$ direction) the bead does not move, so the forces in this direction balance: $N-2m\omega\dot r=0$. Therefore

$$ N=2m\omega\dot r $$

In other words, the normal force of the rod has exactly the magnitude needed to balance the Coriolis force. For example, at the instant when $r=1.0\ \mathrm m$ is reached, $\dot r=r_0\omega\sinh\omega t=0.10\times2.0\times\sqrt{99}\approx1.99\ \mathrm{m/s}$, so $N=2m\times2.0\times1.99\approx8.0\,m\ \mathrm{N}$ ($m$ is the numerical value of the mass measured in kg).

(4) In the inertial frame of reference (polar coordinates, origin O), the angle of the rod is $\theta=\omega t$ ($\dot\theta=\omega$, $\ddot\theta=0$). The only horizontal force on the bead is the normal force $N\bm e_\theta$ perpendicular to the rod, so by the formula for the acceleration in polar coordinates in Chapter 6, the radial component of the equation of motion is

$$ m(\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2)=0\qquad\therefore\quad \ddot r=\omega^2r $$

which agrees with (1). In the $\theta$ direction it is

$$ m(2\dot r\dot\theta+r\ddot\theta)=N\qquad\therefore\quad N=2m\omega\dot r $$

which agrees with (3). The centrifugal term $m\omega^2r$ of the rotating frame is the acceleration term $-r\dot\theta^2$ of the inertial frame moved to the right-hand side, and the Coriolis term corresponds to $2\dot r\dot\theta$.

Exercise 9.4: The speed at which the centrifugal and Coriolis forces become equal on a merry-go-round

Solution

(1) From the period $T=8.0\ \mathrm s$, the angular velocity is

$$ \omega=\frac{2\pi}{T}=\frac{2\pi}{8.0}\approx0.7854\ \mathrm{rad/s} $$

Substituting $m=25\ \mathrm{kg}$ and $\rho=3.0\ \mathrm m$ into the formula of Chapter 9 (the centrifugal force $F_{\text{centrifugal}}=m\omega^2\rho$) gives

$$ F_{\text{centrifugal}}=25\times(0.7854)^2\times3.0\approx25\times0.6169\times3.0\approx46.3\ \mathrm N $$

Its direction is from the axis of rotation toward the child (outward). The child feels an outward force whose magnitude is about 19% of the child's weight (about $25\times9.8\approx245\ \mathrm N$).

(2) When the child moves outward with speed $v$, the magnitude of the Coriolis force is $2m\omega v$. Setting it equal to the centrifugal force $m\omega^2\rho$,

$$ m\omega^2\rho=2m\omega v\qquad\therefore\quad v=\frac{\omega\rho}{2}=\frac{0.7854\times3.0}{2}\approx1.18\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

The mass $m$ cancels, so the result is the same no matter who moves. At an ordinary walking speed (about $1.2\ \mathrm{m/s}$) the Coriolis force is comparable to the centrifugal force, so when you walk on a merry-go-round you are pushed around strongly not only by the centrifugal force but also by the Coriolis force.

Exercise 9.5: Direction of the Coriolis force on an object moving north in the Northern Hemisphere

Solution

Take a local coordinate system with $\bm e_x$ pointing east, $\bm e_y$ north, and $\bm e_z$ vertically upward. By the approximation of Section 9.4.1 (for motion in the horizontal plane, only the vertical component $\Omega_z=\omega_{\text{Earth}}\sin\varphi\ (\gt0,\ \text{Northern Hemisphere})$ of the angular velocity vector of the Earth's rotation matters), $\bm\omega\approx\Omega_z\bm e_z$, and $\bm v_{\text{rel}}=v\bm e_y$ ($v\gt0$, due north). The Coriolis force is

$$ \bm F_{\text{Co}}=-2m\,\bm\omega\times\bm v_{\text{rel}}=-2m\Omega_z v\,(\bm e_z\times\bm e_y) $$

Here, since $\bm e_x,\bm e_y,\bm e_z$ form a right-handed orthonormal basis, $\bm e_z\times\bm e_y=-\bm e_x$ (from the cyclic relations starting from $\bm e_x\times\bm e_y=\bm e_z$). Therefore,

$$ \bm F_{\text{Co}}=-2m\Omega_z v\,(-\bm e_x)=2m\Omega_z v\,\bm e_x $$

so the Coriolis force points east (the $+\bm e_x$ direction). For an object moving due north, east lies on the right-hand side of the direction of motion (north), which agrees with the rule of Theorem 9.2 of Chapter 9 (the rule for a counterclockwise-rotating frame), “the Coriolis force acts to the right of the direction of motion.”

Exercise 9.6: Difference in apparent gravitational acceleration between the equator and the pole

Solution

Using the formula of Chapter 9 ($g_{\text{eff}}(\varphi)\approx g_0-\omega_{\text{Earth}}^2R_{\text{Earth}}\cos^2\varphi$), we have

$$ g_{\text{eff}}(0^\circ)-g_{\text{eff}}(90^\circ) =\left[g_0-\omega_{\text{Earth}}^2R_{\text{Earth}}\cos^20^\circ\right]-\left[g_0-\omega_{\text{Earth}}^2R_{\text{Earth}}\cos^290^\circ\right] $$

Since $\cos0^\circ=1,\ \cos90^\circ=0$, the $g_0$ terms cancel in the difference, and

$$ g_{\text{eff}}(0^\circ)-g_{\text{eff}}(90^\circ)=-\omega_{\text{Earth}}^2R_{\text{Earth}}\times1+\omega_{\text{Earth}}^2R_{\text{Earth}}\times0=-\omega_{\text{Earth}}^2R_{\text{Earth}} $$

The sign indicates that $g_{\text{eff}}$ is smaller at the equator than at the poles (the minus sign means “the equator is smaller”). The magnitude is

$$ \omega_{\text{Earth}}^2R_{\text{Earth}}=(7.292\times10^{-5})^2\times6.371\times10^6\approx0.0339\ \mathrm{m/s^2} $$

That is, the centrifugal effect alone makes the apparent gravitational acceleration at the equator smaller than at the poles by about $0.034\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$. As stated in the Caution in Section 9.4.2, the actually measured difference in gravitational acceleration between the equator and the poles (about $0.052\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$) is larger than this, and the remaining difference of about $0.018\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$ is due to the fact that the Earth is not a perfect sphere but an ellipsoid of revolution bulging in the equatorial direction (the net effect combining the decrease in attraction because the equator is farther from the Earth's center and the increase in attraction due to the mass of the equatorial bulge).

Exercise 9.7: Eastward deflection of a falling object and the Eötvös effect

Solution

The formula of Chapter 9 (the local components of the Coriolis acceleration) is, for $\bm v=(v_{\mathrm E},v_{\mathrm N},v_z)$, eastward $a_{\mathrm E}=2\omega_{\text{Earth}}(\sin\varphi\,v_{\mathrm N}-\cos\varphi\,v_z)$ and upward $a_z=2\omega_{\text{Earth}}\cos\varphi\,v_{\mathrm E}$.

(1) From $h=\tfrac12gt^2$, the fall time is $t=\sqrt{2h/g}=\sqrt{600/9.8}\approx7.82\ \mathrm s$. During the fall the velocity is $v_z\approx-gt$, $v_{\mathrm N}\approx0$, so $a_{\mathrm E}=2\omega_{\text{Earth}}g\cos\varphi\,t$. Integrating twice with respect to time (the initial eastward velocity and displacement are zero),

$$ x_{\mathrm E}=\frac13\,\omega_{\text{Earth}}g\cos\varphi\,t^3 =\frac13\times7.292\times10^{-5}\times9.8\times\cos45^\circ\times(7.82)^3 $$

Substituting $\cos45^\circ\approx0.7071$ and $(7.82)^3\approx478.2\ \mathrm{s^3}$,

$$ x_{\mathrm E}\approx\frac13\times7.146\times10^{-4}\times0.7071\times478.2\approx8.1\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm m $$

that is, the body is displaced eastward by about $8\ \mathrm{cm}$ (the units are $\mathrm{s^{-1}\cdot m/s^2\cdot s^3}=\mathrm m$).

(2) When moving horizontally due east with speed $v=100\ \mathrm{m/s}$ ($v_{\mathrm E}=100\ \mathrm{m/s}$), the upward Coriolis acceleration is

$$ a_z=2\omega_{\text{Earth}}\cos\varphi\,v_{\mathrm E}=2\times7.292\times10^{-5}\times0.7071\times100\approx1.03\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{m/s^2} $$

Since it is directed upward, the apparent gravitational acceleration is decreased by $1.03\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$. Relative to the gravitational acceleration $9.8\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$ the fraction is $1.03\times10^{-2}/9.8\approx1.05\times10^{-3}$; that is, the body becomes about 0.1% lighter. When moving westward, it becomes heavier by the same amount.

Exercise 9.8: Direction of the vortex of a low-pressure system in the Southern Hemisphere

Solution

In the Southern Hemisphere the latitude $\varphi$ is negative ($\varphi\lt0$), so the vertical component of the angular velocity vector of the Earth's rotation, $\Omega_z=\omega_{\text{Earth}}\sin\varphi$, is negative (its direction is opposite to that in the Northern Hemisphere; one may also say that in the Southern Hemisphere the vertical component of the rotation axis as seen from the ground points downward). As found in Section 9.4.5, the direction of the Coriolis force also reverses when the sign of $\Omega_z$ reverses—the Coriolis force that was “to the right of the direction of motion” in the Northern Hemisphere becomes “to the left of the direction of motion” in the Southern Hemisphere.

Therefore, a wind trying to blow in toward a low-pressure system (cyclone) in the Southern Hemisphere is bent to the left again and again. The winds are in equilibrium when the pressure-gradient force (directed toward the center) and the Coriolis force to the left of the direction of motion (directed outward) balance each other, that is, when the air circulates with the center on its right-hand side, and overall the air blows in toward the center while spiraling clockwise (as seen from above). This is exactly opposite to the typhoons and extratropical cyclones of the Northern Hemisphere (counterclockwise). Indeed, cyclones that form near Australia have been observed to spiral clockwise.

← Back to Chapter 9, “Moving Reference Frames and Fictitious Forces — Centrifugal and Coriolis Forces”

Chapter 10 Systems of Particles and Rigid Bodies — Center of Mass, Moment of Inertia, and the Rotational Equation of Motion

Exercise 10.1: Moment of inertia about an axis at $\ell/4$ from one end of a rod

Solution

(1) We carry out the integral in the definition directly. Take a coordinate $r$ ($0\le r\le\ell$) with its origin at one end of the rod; the axis is then at the position $r=\ell/4$. The distance from the axis is $|r-\ell/4|$, and since it is squared, the absolute-value sign is unnecessary:

$$ I=\int_0^{\ell}\left(r-\frac{\ell}{4}\right)^2\frac{M}{\ell}\,\dd r $$

Expanding the integrand gives $\left(r-\dfrac{\ell}{4}\right)^2=r^2-\dfrac{\ell}{2}r+\dfrac{\ell^2}{16}$, so

$$ I=\frac{M}{\ell}\int_0^{\ell}\left(r^2-\frac{\ell}{2}r+\frac{\ell^2}{16}\right)\dd r =\frac{M}{\ell}\left[\frac{r^3}{3}-\frac{\ell}{4}r^2+\frac{\ell^2}{16}r\right]_0^{\ell} $$

Substituting $r=\ell$ gives

$$ I=\frac{M}{\ell}\left(\frac{\ell^3}{3}-\frac{\ell^3}{4}+\frac{\ell^3}{16}\right) =M\ell^2\left(\frac13-\frac14+\frac{1}{16}\right) $$

We bring the fractions to a common denominator (take the denominator to be 48: $\frac13=\frac{16}{48},\ \frac14=\frac{12}{48},\ \frac{1}{16}=\frac{3}{48}$):

$$ \frac13-\frac14+\frac1{16}=\frac{16-12+3}{48}=\frac{7}{48} $$

Therefore,

$$ I=\frac{7}{48}M\ell^2 $$

(2) A method using the parallel-axis theorem. The distance from the center of mass ($r=\ell/2$) to the axis ($r=\ell/4$) is $d=\ell/2-\ell/4=\ell/4$. Using Theorem 10.3 of Chapter 10 ($I=I_{\mathrm{cm}}+Md^2$) and the moment of inertia about the center of mass, $I_{\mathrm{cm}}=\dfrac{1}{12}M\ell^2$ (Section 10.4), we obtain

$$ I=\frac{1}{12}M\ell^2+M\left(\frac{\ell}{4}\right)^2=\frac{1}{12}M\ell^2+\frac{1}{16}M\ell^2 $$

Bringing the fractions to a common denominator (48): since $\dfrac{1}{12}=\dfrac{4}{48},\ \dfrac{1}{16}=\dfrac{3}{48}$,

$$ I=\frac{4+3}{48}M\ell^2=\frac{7}{48}M\ell^2 $$

which agrees exactly with the result of the direct integration in (1).

Exercise 10.2: Moment of inertia of a rod of nonuniform density

Solution

First, we find the total mass $M$ from the linear density $\rho(r)=\rho_0\left(1+\dfrac{r}{\ell}\right)$:

$$ M=\int_0^{\ell}\rho(r)\,\dd r=\rho_0\int_0^{\ell}\left(1+\frac{r}{\ell}\right)\dd r =\rho_0\left[r+\frac{r^2}{2\ell}\right]_0^{\ell}=\rho_0\left(\ell+\frac{\ell}{2}\right)=\frac{3}{2}\rho_0\ell $$

Solving this for $\rho_0$ gives

$$ \rho_0=\frac{2M}{3\ell} $$

Next, we compute the moment of inertia about one end ($r=0$):

$$ I=\int_0^{\ell}r^2\rho(r)\,\dd r=\rho_0\int_0^{\ell}r^2\left(1+\frac{r}{\ell}\right)\dd r =\rho_0\int_0^{\ell}\left(r^2+\frac{r^3}{\ell}\right)\dd r $$ $$ =\rho_0\left[\frac{r^3}{3}+\frac{r^4}{4\ell}\right]_0^{\ell}=\rho_0\left(\frac{\ell^3}{3}+\frac{\ell^3}{4}\right)=\rho_0\ell^3\left(\frac13+\frac14\right)=\rho_0\ell^3\cdot\frac{7}{12}=\frac{7}{12}\rho_0\ell^3 $$

Finally, we substitute $\rho_0=\dfrac{2M}{3\ell}$:

$$ I=\frac{7}{12}\cdot\frac{2M}{3\ell}\cdot\ell^3=\frac{14}{36}M\ell^2=\frac{7}{18}M\ell^2 $$

Compared with a rod of uniform density (the result of Chapter 10, $I_{\text{end}}=\dfrac13M\ell^2=\dfrac{6}{18}M\ell^2$), $\dfrac{7}{18}M\ell^2$ is slightly larger—this is because the density $\rho(r)=\rho_0(1+r/\ell)$ becomes larger the closer one gets to the side far from the end ($r=0$), namely $r=\ell$ (the linear density doubles), so that, even for the same total mass, more of the mass is distributed on the side far from the axis, which has the effect of increasing the moment of inertia.

Exercise 10.3: Deriving the moments of inertia of a ring and a cylinder

Solution

(1) Ring about its central axis. The total circumference of the ring is $2\pi R$, so the linear density is $\lambda=\dfrac{M}{2\pi R}$. Dividing the ring into small pieces of length $\dd s$ (mass $\dd m=\lambda\,\dd s$), every piece lies at distance $R$ from the axis of rotation (the axis through the center of the circle perpendicular to its plane), so by Definition 10.3,

$$ I=\int R^2\,\dd m=R^2\int\dd m=R^2M=MR^2 $$

(We used the total mass $\int\dd m=M$. One does not even need to integrate: the result follows immediately from the fact that the distance from the axis is $R$ everywhere.)

(2) Cylinder about its central axis. Slice the cylinder (radius $R$, length $h$) into disks of thickness $\dd z$ perpendicular to the central axis. The mass of a single disk is $\dd m=\dfrac{M}{h}\dd z$, and by the derivation for the disk in Section 10.6, the moment of inertia of this disk about its central axis is $\tfrac12\,\dd m\,R^2$. Adding them all up,

$$ I=\int\frac12R^2\,\dd m=\frac12R^2\int\dd m=\frac12MR^2 $$

We obtain the same value as for a disk, regardless of the length $h$ of the cylinder.

(3) Cylinder about a diameter. Take the center of the cylinder as the origin, the central axis as the $z$ axis, and a diametral direction as the $x$ axis. The mass of the disk of thickness $\dd z$ at height $z$ ($-h/2\le z\le h/2$) from the center is $\dd m=\dfrac{M}{h}\dd z$, and by the perpendicular-axis theorem the moment of inertia of this disk about a diameter through its own center (center of mass) is $\tfrac14\,\dd m\,R^2$ (Section 10.7.2). The $x$ axis is a parallel axis at distance $z$ from the center of mass of this disk, so by the parallel-axis theorem (Theorem 10.3), the moment of inertia of this disk about the $x$ axis is

$$ \dd I=\frac14\,\dd m\,R^2+\dd m\,z^2=\left(\frac14R^2+z^2\right)\frac{M}{h}\,\dd z $$

We integrate this from $z=-h/2$ to $h/2$:

$$ I=\frac{M}{h}\int_{-h/2}^{h/2}\left(\frac14R^2+z^2\right)\dd z=\frac{M}{h}\left[\frac14R^2z+\frac{z^3}{3}\right]_{-h/2}^{h/2} =\frac{M}{h}\left(\frac14R^2h+\frac{h^3}{12}\right) $$

(The difference between the upper and lower limits of $z^3/3$ is $2\cdot\dfrac{(h/2)^3}{3}=\dfrac{h^3}{12}$, and the difference for the first term is $\dfrac14R^2\cdot h$.) Therefore,

$$ I=\frac14MR^2+\frac1{12}Mh^2 $$

This is the result. (Check: in the limit $h\to0$ of a thin disk it gives $\tfrac14MR^2$ (the disk about a diameter), and in the limit $R\to0$ of a thin rod it gives $\tfrac1{12}Mh^2$ (the rod about its center of mass, Section 10.4.2), so it agrees with both known results.)

Exercise 10.4: Condition for the torques of internal forces to cancel

Solution

(1) Substituting the law of action and reaction $\bm F_{21}=-\bm F_{12}$, we obtain

$$ \bm r_1\times\bm F_{12}+\bm r_2\times\bm F_{21}=\bm r_1\times\bm F_{12}-\bm r_2\times\bm F_{12}=(\bm r_1-\bm r_2)\times\bm F_{12} $$

(The cross product satisfies the distributive law with respect to its first factor.)

(2) When the internal forces lie along the line joining the two particles, $\bm F_{12}$ is parallel to $\bm r_1-\bm r_2$ ($\bm F_{12}=f\,(\bm r_1-\bm r_2)$, where $f$ is a scalar). Since the cross product of parallel vectors is $\bm 0$,

$$ (\bm r_1-\bm r_2)\times\bm F_{12}=f\,(\bm r_1-\bm r_2)\times(\bm r_1-\bm r_2)=\bm 0 $$

Universal gravitation, the Coulomb force, and the elastic force of a spring all act in a direction along the line joining the two particles (and their magnitudes are functions of the distance alone), so they satisfy this condition. Therefore the torques (moments of force) of the internal forces cancel pair by pair, and for the system of particles as a whole $\dd\bm L/\dd t=\bm N_{\mathrm{ext}}$ (the sum of the torques of the external forces) holds.

(3) For example, let $\bm r_1-\bm r_2=(\ell,0,0)$ and take the internal forces to be $\bm F_{12}=(0,f,0)$, $\bm F_{21}=(0,-f,0)$ (equal in magnitude and opposite in direction, so they satisfy the law of action and reaction; but the forces are perpendicular to the line joining the two particles (the $x$ direction)). Then, by the result of (1), the sum of the torques of the internal forces is

$$ (\bm r_1-\bm r_2)\times\bm F_{12}=(\ell,0,0)\times(0,f,0)=(0,0,\ell f)\neq\bm 0 $$

which is not zero (we used the components of the cross product, $(a_yb_z-a_zb_y,\ a_zb_x-a_xb_z,\ a_xb_y-a_yb_x)$, and the $z$ component is $\ell f-0=\ell f$). In other words, the forces that the two particles exert on each other alone make the angular momentum of the whole system change at the rate $\dd L_z/\dd t=\ell f$. Therefore, for the conservation of angular momentum (the vanishing of the torques of the internal forces) to hold, the condition that “the internal forces lie along the line joining the two particles” is needed in addition to the law of action and reaction (this is called the strong form of the law of action and reaction). Note that some forces, such as the electromagnetic force between moving charged particles, do not satisfy this condition (in that case, angular momentum is conserved once the angular momentum carried by the electromagnetic field is included; this is studied in Part V).

Exercise 10.5: Acceleration of a hollow cylinder rolling down an incline

Solution

We use the general formula for the acceleration of rolling motion obtained in Chapter 10, $a=\dfrac{g\sin\alpha}{1+c}$ ($I=cMR^2$, where $c$ is a dimensionless coefficient determined by the shape). A thin-walled hollow cylinder has $I=MR^2$, so $c=1$, and

$$ a_{\text{hollow cyl.}}=\frac{g\sin\alpha}{1+1}=\frac12g\sin\alpha $$

On the other hand, as found in Chapter 10, a uniform solid sphere has $c=2/5$ and $a_{\text{sphere}}=\dfrac57g\sin\alpha\approx0.714\,g\sin\alpha$, and a uniform solid cylinder has $c=1/2$ and $a_{\text{solid cyl.}}=\dfrac23g\sin\alpha\approx0.667\,g\sin\alpha$. Comparing the magnitudes of the numbers,

$$ a_{\text{sphere}}\left(\frac57\approx0.714\right)\ \gt\ a_{\text{solid cyl.}}\left(\frac23\approx0.667\right)\ \gt\ a_{\text{hollow cyl.}}\left(\frac12=0.5\right) $$

and the larger the acceleration, the sooner the body finishes descending the incline. Therefore, if the bodies roll down the same incline at the same time, the solid sphere reaches the bottom of the incline first, the solid cylinder next, and the hollow cylinder last. This corresponds to the fact that for a shape with a smaller $c=I/(MR^2)$ (a dimensionless coefficient expressing the magnitude of the moment of inertia for the same mass and radius), the mass is concentrated nearer the axis of rotation and a smaller fraction of the energy is taken up by the rotation, so the translational speed increases faster ($c$ is smallest for the solid sphere and largest for the hollow cylinder, whose mass is all concentrated on the outer circumference, the place farthest from the axis).

Exercise 10.6: Position of the axis that minimizes the moment of inertia of a rod

Solution

We use the parallel-axis theorem (Theorem 10.3 of Chapter 10, $I=I_{\mathrm{cm}}+Md^2$). Describe the position of the axis by the distance $x_0$ measured from one end ($0\le x_0\le\ell$); the distance from the center of mass ($x=\ell/2$) to the axis ($x=x_0$) is then $|x_0-\ell/2|$, and since it is squared, the absolute value is unnecessary:

$$ I(x_0)=I_{\mathrm{cm}}+M\left(x_0-\frac{\ell}{2}\right)^2=\frac{1}{12}M\ell^2+M\left(x_0-\frac{\ell}{2}\right)^2 $$

Differentiating $I(x_0)$ with respect to $x_0$,

$$ \frac{\dd I}{\dd x_0}=2M\left(x_0-\frac{\ell}{2}\right) $$

Setting this equal to 0,

$$ 2M\left(x_0-\frac{\ell}{2}\right)=0\qquad\therefore\ x_0=\frac{\ell}{2} $$

(Since $M\neq0$, we may divide by $M$.) The second derivative is $\dfrac{\dd^2I}{\dd x_0^2}=2M\gt0$ (a constant, always positive), so $x_0=\ell/2$ gives a local minimum (in this case, since $I(x_0)$ is a quadratic function of $x_0$—a parabola opening upward—this local minimum is at the same time the minimum over the whole interval $[0,\ell]$). The minimum value itself is

$$ I\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{12}M\ell^2+M\cdot0^2=\frac{1}{12}M\ell^2=I_{\mathrm{cm}} $$

which is precisely the moment of inertia about the center of mass. This expresses a result that is natural physically—in the parallel-axis theorem $I=I_{\mathrm{cm}}+Md^2$, $d^2\ge0$ is always nonnegative, so $I(x_0)\ge I_{\mathrm{cm}}$ always holds, and equality (the minimum) is attained only when the axis passes through the center of mass ($x_0=\ell/2$, that is, the distance between the axis and the center of mass is zero). In other words, for any body, whichever axis it is rotated about, the moment of inertia about the axis through the center of mass is the smallest among all parallel axes—this is nothing other than a generalization of the concrete example seen in Section 10.4, that “it is harder to rotate about an end ($I=M\ell^2/3$) than about the center of mass ($I=M\ell^2/12$).”

Exercise 10.7: Distribution of the kinetic energy of a rolling body

Solution

Let $v$ be the speed of the center of mass and $\omega$ the angular velocity. The condition for rolling without slipping (the rolling-contact condition of Section 10.9.1) is $v=R\omega$. Writing the moment of inertia as $I=cMR^2$, the translational kinetic energy and the rotational kinetic energy are

$$ K_{\mathrm{trans}}=\frac12Mv^2,\qquad K_{\mathrm{rot}}=\frac12I\omega^2=\frac12\,cMR^2\left(\frac{v}{R}\right)^2=\frac12\,c\,Mv^2 $$

(substituting $\omega=v/R$ cancels $R^2$). Therefore the ratio, independent of the shape, is

$$ \frac{K_{\mathrm{rot}}}{K_{\mathrm{trans}}}=c $$

which is determined by $c$ alone.

(1) Disk. Since $c=1/2$, $K_{\mathrm{trans}}:K_{\mathrm{rot}}=1:\tfrac12=2:1$. Of the total kinetic energy $K=K_{\mathrm{trans}}+K_{\mathrm{rot}}=\tfrac32K_{\mathrm{trans}}$, the fraction taken up by rotation is $\dfrac{1/2}{1+1/2}=\dfrac13$.

(2) Solid sphere and hollow cylinder. The solid sphere has $c=2/5$, so $K_{\mathrm{trans}}:K_{\mathrm{rot}}=1:\tfrac25=5:2$, and the fraction taken up by rotation is $\dfrac{2/5}{1+2/5}=\dfrac27\approx0.286$. The hollow cylinder (ring) has $c=1$, so $K_{\mathrm{trans}}:K_{\mathrm{rot}}=1:1$, and the fraction taken up by rotation is $\dfrac{1}{1+1}=\dfrac12$. To summarize, the fraction taken up by rotation increases in the order solid sphere ($2/7\approx0.29$) $\lt$ disk ($1/3\approx0.33$) $\lt$ ring ($1/2$). The farther from the axis the mass is distributed (the larger $c$ is), the more energy is diverted to rotation for the same speed of advance, and the smaller the acceleration when descending an incline (Section 10.9).

Exercise 10.8: Pivot position that minimizes the period of a physical pendulum

Solution

(1) The pivot is a point at distance $d$ from the center of mass. From the parallel-axis theorem (Theorem 10.3) and the moment of inertia of the rod about its center of mass, $I_{\mathrm{cm}}=M\ell^2/12$, the moment of inertia about the pivot is $I=\dfrac{M\ell^2}{12}+Md^2$. By Theorem 10.7,

$$ T(d)=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{I}{Mgd}}=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{\ell^2/12+d^2}{g\,d}} $$

(2) Minimizing $T$ is equivalent to minimizing the quantity under the square root, $f(d)=\dfrac{\ell^2/12+d^2}{d}=\dfrac{\ell^2}{12d}+d$. Differentiating with respect to $d$,

$$ f'(d)=-\frac{\ell^2}{12d^2}+1 $$

From $f'(d)=0$ we get $d^2=\ell^2/12$, that is, $d=\dfrac{\ell}{\sqrt{12}}=\dfrac{\ell}{2\sqrt3}\approx0.289\,\ell$. Since the second derivative $f''(d)=\dfrac{\ell^2}{6d^3}\gt0$, this is a local minimum (and the minimum). At this point $f=\dfrac{\ell^2}{12}\cdot\dfrac{\sqrt{12}}{\ell}+\dfrac{\ell}{\sqrt{12}}=\dfrac{2\ell}{\sqrt{12}}=\dfrac{\ell}{\sqrt3}$, so the minimum period is $T_{\min}=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{\ell}{\sqrt3\,g}}$. (At this point the length of the equivalent simple pendulum is $\ell_{\mathrm{eq}}=d+\dfrac{\ell^2}{12d}=2d=\dfrac{\ell}{\sqrt3}$, exactly twice the distance $d$ from the pivot to the center of mass.)

(3) Substituting $\ell=1\ \mathrm m$ and $g=9.8\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$,

$$ T_{\min}=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{1}{\sqrt3\times9.8}}=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{1}{16.97}}=2\pi\times0.2428\approx1.53\ \mathrm s $$

For the case in which one end is used as the pivot ($d=\ell/2=0.5\ \mathrm m$),

$$ T=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{1/12+0.25}{9.8\times0.5}}=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{0.3333}{4.9}}=2\pi\times0.2608\approx1.64\ \mathrm s $$

Taking the pivot at the optimal position (about $0.29\ \mathrm m$ from the center of mass) makes the period about $7\%$ shorter than using an end as the pivot. If the pivot is brought too close to the center of mass ($d\to0$), the restoring torque becomes small and the period becomes arbitrarily long.

Exercise 10.9: Center of mass of a disk with a hole

Solution

Since the disk is uniform, mass is proportional to area. Let $M_0$ be the mass of the disk (radius $R$) before the hole is cut out. From the area ratio $\dfrac{\pi(R/2)^2}{\pi R^2}=\dfrac14$, the mass of the disk-shaped piece corresponding to the hole (radius $R/2$) is $m_{\text{hole}}=\dfrac14M_0$. The mass of the remaining plate is $M=M_0-m_{\text{hole}}=\dfrac34M_0$.

Take $O$ as the origin and the $x$ axis in the direction of the center of the hole. The center of mass of the disk before cutting is at $x=0$, the center of mass of the disk-shaped piece corresponding to the hole is its center $x=R/2$, and the center of mass of the remaining plate is denoted by $x_{\mathrm{cm}}$. The disk before cutting is made up of two parts, the “remaining plate” and the “disk-shaped piece corresponding to the hole,” so applying Definition 10.1 (the $x$ coordinate) to the two parts separately,

$$ 0=\frac{M\,x_{\mathrm{cm}}+m_{\text{hole}}\cdot\dfrac R2}{M_0} $$

(the left-hand side is the center of mass $x=0$ of the disk before cutting). Solving this for $x_{\mathrm{cm}}$:

$$ M\,x_{\mathrm{cm}}=-m_{\text{hole}}\cdot\frac R2\qquad\therefore\ x_{\mathrm{cm}}=-\frac{\frac14M_0}{\frac34M_0}\cdot\frac R2=-\frac13\cdot\frac R2=-\frac R6 $$

The center of mass lies on the side opposite the hole (the negative side of $x$), at a distance $R/6$ from the center (since the mass of the hole is missing, the center of mass shifts away from the hole. The same result is obtained by the method of “regarding the hole as a negative mass and using the definition of the center of mass,” writing $x_{\mathrm{cm}}=\dfrac{M_0\cdot0-m_{\text{hole}}\cdot R/2}{M_0-m_{\text{hole}}}$).

← Back to Chapter 10, “Systems of Particles and Rigid Bodies — Center of Mass, Moment of Inertia, and the Rotational Equation of Motion”

Chapter 11 Basic Properties of Waves — The Equation of a Wave, Interference, Standing Waves, and Diffraction

Exercise 11.1: Energy conservation for simple harmonic motion with a shifted equilibrium position

Solution

Multiplying both sides of the equation of motion $m\ddot x=-\kappa(x-x_0)$ ($x_0$ is a constant) by the velocity $\dot x=v$, we obtain

$$ m\dot v\cdot v=-\kappa(x-x_0)\cdot v \qquad\text{that is}\qquad mv\frac{\dd v}{\dd t}=-\kappa(x-x_0)\frac{\dd x}{\dd t} $$

By the rule for differentiating a composite function, the left-hand side is equal to $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}\!\left(\dfrac12mv^2\right)=mv\dfrac{\dd v}{\dd t}$. For the right-hand side as well, noting that $x_0$ is a constant, the following equality holds:

$$ \frac{\dd}{\dd t}\left[\frac12\kappa(x-x_0)^2\right]=\kappa(x-x_0)\cdot\frac{\dd}{\dd t}(x-x_0)=\kappa(x-x_0)\cdot\left(\frac{\dd x}{\dd t}-0\right)=\kappa(x-x_0)\frac{\dd x}{\dd t} $$

(Differentiating $x_0$ as a constant gives $0$, so $\dd(x-x_0)/\dd t=\dd x/\dd t$.) Therefore,

$$ \frac{\dd}{\dd t}\left(\frac12mv^2\right)=-\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\left[\frac12\kappa(x-x_0)^2\right] \qquad\therefore\qquad \frac{\dd}{\dd t}\left[\frac12mv^2+\frac12\kappa(x-x_0)^2\right]=0 $$

holds. That the time derivative is identically $0$ means that the quantity inside the brackets is constant, independent of time, so, using a constant of integration $E$,

$$ \frac12mv^2+\frac12\kappa(x-x_0)^2=E $$

holds (this is exactly the same procedure as the derivation for the case $x_0=0$ in Section 11.1 of the main text, except that the equilibrium position is displaced by $x_0$).

Exercise 11.2: Checking that a wave traveling to the left satisfies the wave equation

Solution

Let $g$ be an arbitrary (twice differentiable) function of one variable, let $w\equiv x+vt$, and consider $y(x,t)=g(w)=g(x+vt)$. Here $w$ is a function of $x,t$, with $\pdiff{w}{x}=1,\ \pdiff{w}{t}=v$ (note that the sign is opposite to that of $\partial u/\partial t=-v$ for $u=x-vt$). By the formula for partial derivatives of a composite function,

$$ \pdiff{y}{t}=\frac{\dd g}{\dd w}\cdot\pdiff{w}{t}=g'(w)\cdot v=vg'(w) $$

Differentiating partially with respect to $t$ once more, since $g'(w)$ is also a composite function that depends on $t$ through $w$,

$$ \pdiff{^2y}{t^2}=\pdiff{}{t}\bigl[vg'(w)\bigr]=v\cdot g''(w)\cdot\pdiff{w}{t}=v\cdot g''(w)\cdot v=v^2g''(w) $$

we obtain (in the case of $u$ the factor $-v$ was multiplied twice, so the signs canceled and gave $+v^2$; here the factor $+v$ is multiplied twice, so again we get $+v^2$—we see that even though the signs of the first derivatives are opposite, the second derivatives have the same sign). On the other hand, differentiating partially with respect to $x$, since $\partial w/\partial x=1$, we have

$$ \pdiff{y}{x}=g'(w),\qquad \pdiff{^2y}{x^2}=g''(w)\cdot\pdiff{w}{x}=g''(w) $$

Therefore,

$$ \pdiff{^2y}{t^2}=v^2g''(w)=v^2\pdiff{^2y}{x^2} $$

and we have confirmed that $y=g(x+vt)$ also satisfies the one-dimensional wave equation (regardless of the specific form of $g$). Together with the case $y=f(x-vt)$ in the main text (Section 11.2), this verifies that both terms of the general solution $y=f(x-vt)+g(x+vt)$ satisfy the wave equation.

Exercise 11.3: Determining the direction of propagation from the coefficients of the wave function

Solution

Comparing $y=0.03\sin(15t-3x)\ [\mathrm{m}]$ with the form $y=A\sin(\alpha t+\beta x+\phi)$ given in Theorem 11.2 of Chapter 11, we find

$$ A=0.03\ \mathrm{m},\qquad \alpha=15\ \mathrm{rad/s},\qquad \beta=-3\ \mathrm{rad/m},\qquad \phi=0 $$

(since $-3x=+(-3)x$, we have $\beta=-3$). The product of the coefficients is

$$ \alpha\beta=15\times(-3)=-45\lt 0 $$

By Theorem 11.2, when $\alpha\beta\lt 0$ the wave travels in the positive direction of the $x$ axis. From the relation $\dot x=-\alpha/\beta$ in the proof of Theorem 11.2, the phase velocity is

$$ \dot x=-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}=-\frac{15}{-3}=5\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

Since the sign is positive, this is, as expected, consistent with the wave traveling in the $+x$ direction. The speed is $5\ \mathrm{m/s}$ (this wave has exactly the form $y=A\sin(\omega t-kx)$ with $\omega=15\ \mathrm{rad/s}$ and $k=3\ \mathrm{rad/m}$, and computing the phase velocity directly as $v=\omega/k=15/3=5\ \mathrm{m/s}$ gives the same value).

Exercise 11.4: Interference condition for two wave sources with a phase difference of $\pi/2$

Solution

The phase difference (the phase of $y_B$ $-$ the phase of $y_A$) between $y_A=a_A\sin\{\omega(t-\overline{\mathrm{AP}}/v)+\phi\}$ and $y_B=a_B\sin\{\omega(t-\overline{\mathrm{BP}}/v)+\phi+\pi/2\}$ is, as in the main text (Section 11.6.1),

$$ \Delta\theta=\frac{\omega}{v}\bigl(\overline{\mathrm{AP}}-\overline{\mathrm{BP}}\bigr)+\frac{\pi}{2}=\frac{2\pi}{\lambda}\bigl(\overline{\mathrm{AP}}-\overline{\mathrm{BP}}\bigr)+\frac{\pi}{2} $$

(the first term is the phase difference due to the difference in the distances traveled by the waves, and the second term is the phase difference of the sources themselves). The waves reinforce each other when the total phase difference $\Delta\theta$ is $2n\pi$, and cancel each other when it is $(2n+1)\pi$ (the “Key point” box in Section 11.6.1).

Condition for reinforcement: From $\Delta\theta=2n\pi$ ($n\in\mathbb{Z}$),

$$ \frac{2\pi}{\lambda}\bigl(\overline{\mathrm{AP}}-\overline{\mathrm{BP}}\bigr)=2n\pi-\frac{\pi}{2} \qquad\therefore\quad \overline{\mathrm{AP}}-\overline{\mathrm{BP}}=\left(n-\frac14\right)\lambda $$

Condition for cancellation: From $\Delta\theta=(2n+1)\pi$,

$$ \frac{2\pi}{\lambda}\bigl(\overline{\mathrm{AP}}-\overline{\mathrm{BP}}\bigr)=(2n+1)\pi-\frac{\pi}{2}=2n\pi+\frac{\pi}{2} \qquad\therefore\quad \overline{\mathrm{AP}}-\overline{\mathrm{BP}}=\left(n+\frac14\right)\lambda $$

These are intermediate conditions, neither those for sources in phase (reinforcement at $n\lambda$, cancellation at $(n+1/2)\lambda$) nor those for sources in antiphase (reinforcement at $(n-1/2)\lambda$, cancellation at $n\lambda$). For example, for $n=0$, the waves reinforce each other at points where $\overline{\mathrm{AP}}-\overline{\mathrm{BP}}=-\lambda/4$ (points where $\mathrm{B}$ is farther away than $\mathrm{A}$ by $\lambda/4$), and cancel each other at the points where the difference is $+\lambda/4$. We can understand this as follows: because the phase of source $\mathrm{B}$ is advanced, the wave from $\mathrm{B}$ comes to be in phase with the wave from $\mathrm{A}$ only after it has traveled an extra $\lambda/4$.

Exercise 11.5: Superposition of two oppositely traveling sinusoidal waves with a phase offset

Solution

Let $y_1=A\sin(\omega t-kx)$ and $y_2=A\sin(\omega t+kx+\pi)$. First, we simplify $y_2$ using $\sin(\theta+\pi)=-\sin\theta$ (the periodicity of the sine; see the “Key point” box in Section 11.6.1), which gives

$$ y_2=A\sin(\omega t+kx+\pi)=-A\sin(\omega t+kx) $$

Therefore,

$$ y=y_1+y_2=A\sin(\omega t-kx)-A\sin(\omega t+kx) $$

holds. Now we expand each of $y_1,\ -y_2'$ ($y_2'\equiv A\sin(\omega t+kx)$) using the addition formulas (the same procedure as in Section 11.7):

$$ A\sin(\omega t-kx)=A(\sin\omega t\cos kx-\cos\omega t\sin kx) $$ $$ A\sin(\omega t+kx)=A(\sin\omega t\cos kx+\cos\omega t\sin kx) $$

Subtracting side by side ($y=$ the upper expression $-$ the lower expression), the terms in $\sin\omega t\cos kx$ cancel each other, and

$$ y=A(\sin\omega t\cos kx-\cos\omega t\sin kx)-A(\sin\omega t\cos kx+\cos\omega t\sin kx)=-2A\cos\omega t\sin kx $$

is obtained. Therefore,

$$ y=-2A\cos\omega t\sin kx $$

holds. This differs from the main text's $y=2A\sin\omega t\cos kx$ (a combination of $\sin\omega t$ and $\cos kx$): it is a standing wave in which $\cos\omega t$ and $\sin kx$ appear in place of those. Since it is again completely separated into a part depending on time and a part depending on space, this too is a standing wave. In the main text, the positions of the nodes (where the amplitude is always $0$) were given by $\cos kx=0$ ($x=(2m+1)\lambda/4$), whereas here they are the positions where $\sin kx=0$ ($kx=m\pi$, that is, $x=m\lambda/2$)—exactly interchanged with the positions of the antinodes in the main text's case. Likewise, the positions of the antinodes coincide with the positions of the nodes in the main text ($x=(2m+1)\lambda/4$). In other words, shifting the phase of the source by $\pi$ shifts the nodes and antinodes of the standing wave by exactly half of a half wavelength ($\lambda/4$).

Exercise 11.6: Gap width and whether diffraction occurs

Solution

By Definition 11.5 of Chapter 11 and the Caution in Section 11.9, diffraction occurs regardless of the width of the gap, but the spreading of the wave around becomes pronounced when the gap width is comparable to the wavelength or smaller. The width of the gap between the buildings is $20\ \mathrm{m}$.

FM radio waves ($\lambda\approx3\ \mathrm{m}$): The gap width ($20\ \mathrm{m}$) is much larger than the wavelength ($3\ \mathrm{m}$) (about $20/3\approx6.7$ times as large). Therefore, this falls outside the condition for diffraction to become “pronounced” in Definition 11.5; FM radio waves do not spread around much and tend to weaken in the shadow of buildings (indeed, it is known that FM broadcasts and television signals are received poorly in the shadow of buildings).

AM radio waves ($\lambda\approx300\ \mathrm{m}$): The gap width ($20\ \mathrm{m}$) is much smaller than the wavelength ($300\ \mathrm{m}$) (the ratio is about $20/300\approx0.067$, so the gap width is less than one tenth of the wavelength). Since the gap width is far smaller than the wavelength, the condition of Definition 11.5 is well satisfied, and AM radio waves spread around well and are comparatively easy to receive even in the shadow of buildings.

In summary, AM radio waves, which have a long wavelength, spread around better at the same gap between buildings than FM radio waves, which have a short wavelength. This is the same principle as the phenomenon seen in Example 11.11 of Chapter 11—sound (long wavelength) can be heard well even behind a wall, whereas light (extremely short wavelength) casts a shadow behind a wall—that is, the ratio of the wavelength to the width of the gap (or of the obstacle) determines how conspicuous diffraction is.

Exercise 11.7: Interference of two waves with different amplitudes

Solution

(1) By Formula 11.4, the amplitude of the resultant wave is $a=\sqrt{a_A^2+a_B^2+2a_Aa_B\cos\Delta\theta}$. Substituting $a_A=3.0\ \mathrm{mm},\ a_B=1.0\ \mathrm{mm},\ \Delta\theta=\pi/3$ ($\cos(\pi/3)=1/2$), we obtain

$$ a=\sqrt{3.0^2+1.0^2+2\times3.0\times1.0\times\dfrac12}\ \mathrm{mm}=\sqrt{9+1+3}\ \mathrm{mm}=\sqrt{13}\ \mathrm{mm}\approx3.6\ \mathrm{mm} $$

(2) For $\Delta\theta=0$ ($\cos0=1$), $a=\sqrt{9+1+6}\ \mathrm{mm}=\sqrt{16}\ \mathrm{mm}=4.0\ \mathrm{mm}$, which is equal to $a_A+a_B$ (the waves reinforce each other). For $\Delta\theta=\pi$ ($\cos\pi=-1$), $a=\sqrt{9+1-6}\ \mathrm{mm}=\sqrt{4}\ \mathrm{mm}=2.0\ \mathrm{mm}$, which is equal to $|a_A-a_B|$ (the waves cancel each other). Therefore the $3.6\ \mathrm{mm}$ in (1) lies between $2.0\ \mathrm{mm}$ and $4.0\ \mathrm{mm}$. As the phase difference increases from $0$ to $\pi$, the resultant amplitude decreases smoothly from $4.0\ \mathrm{mm}$ to $2.0\ \mathrm{mm}$ (because $\cos\Delta\theta$ decreases from $1$ to $-1$).

Exercise 11.8: Construction using Huygens's principle

Solution

(1) Consider the moment when one end $\mathrm{O}$ of the wavefront of the incident wave (the segment $\mathrm{OQ}$, inclined at an angle $\theta_i$ to the boundary) reaches the boundary (a horizontal straight line). Let $\Delta t$ be the time it takes the other end $\mathrm{Q}$ to reach the point $\mathrm{R}$ on the boundary, and let $v$ be the speed of the wave; then $\overline{\mathrm{QR}}=v\Delta t$. The elementary wavelet emitted from $\mathrm{O}$ during this time spreads at speed $v$ in the same medium and becomes a semicircle of radius $v\Delta t$ centered at $\mathrm{O}$. If we draw the tangent from $\mathrm{R}$ to this semicircle and let $\mathrm{T}$ be the point of tangency, then $\mathrm{RT}$ is the wavefront of the reflected wave. The right triangles $\mathrm{OQR}$ (right angle at $\mathrm{Q}$) and $\mathrm{RTO}$ (right angle at $\mathrm{T}$) share the hypotenuse $\mathrm{OR}$ and have $\overline{\mathrm{QR}}=\overline{\mathrm{OT}}=v\Delta t$, so they are congruent. Hence $\angle\mathrm{QOR}=\angle\mathrm{TRO}$; that is, the angle between the incident wavefront and the boundary is equal to the angle between the reflected wavefront and the boundary. Since a wavefront is perpendicular to the direction of travel and the boundary is perpendicular to the normal, these angles are equal to the angle of incidence $\theta_i$ and the angle of reflection $\theta_r$, respectively, and therefore $\theta_i=\theta_r$.

(2) When a plane wave passes through a gap of width $a=\lambda/2$, each point lined up in the gap becomes a source of an elementary wavelet. However, since the gap width is smaller than the wavelength, the whole gap may be regarded as a single point source, and elementary wavelets (wavefronts) in the form of semicircles of radius $v\Delta t,\ 2v\Delta t,\dots$ centered at the center of the gap are formed. The wavefront tangent to all of these elementary wavelets is a semicircle centered on the gap (a cross section of a spherical wave). Therefore, the wave also goes around into the shadow of the plate and spreads almost uniformly over a wide region behind the gap (this is the same situation as in Figure 11.15 of the main text).

Exercise 11.9: Change of wavelength on refraction

Solution

(1) The frequency does not change across the boundary, so it remains $f=25\ \mathrm{Hz}$. From $v=f\lambda$,

$$ \lambda_A=\frac{v_A}{f}=\frac{8.0\ \mathrm{m/s}}{25\ \mathrm{Hz}}=0.32\ \mathrm{m},\qquad \lambda_B=\frac{v_B}{f}=\frac{5.0\ \mathrm{m/s}}{25\ \mathrm{Hz}}=0.20\ \mathrm{m} $$

(the ratio $\lambda_A/\lambda_B=0.32/0.20=1.6$ agrees with $v_A/v_B=8.0/5.0=1.6$).

(2) By the law of refraction $\dfrac{\sin\theta_i}{\sin\theta_t}=\dfrac{v_A}{v_B}$,

$$ \sin\theta_t=\sin\theta_i\times\frac{v_B}{v_A}=\sin30^\circ\times\frac{5.0}{8.0}=0.5\times0.625=0.3125 $$ $$ \therefore\quad\theta_t=\arcsin(0.3125)\approx18.2^\circ $$

Since the wave enters the slower medium $B$, the angle of refraction $18.2^\circ$ is smaller than the angle of incidence $30^\circ$, and the wave bends toward the normal.

Exercise 11.10: Intensity of a spherical wave and distance

Solution

(1) The energy passing per second through a sphere of radius $r$ centered on the sound source (area $4\pi r^2$) is equal to the total power $P$ of the source (since no energy is lost). The intensity is the power per unit area, so $P=I\times4\pi r^2$.

$$ P=4.0\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{W/m^2}\times4\pi\times(10\ \mathrm m)^2=4.0\times10^{-6}\times400\pi\ \mathrm W\approx5.0\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm W $$

(the units agree: $\mathrm{W/m^2}\times\mathrm{m^2}=\mathrm W$).

(2) The same total power $P$ spreads over a sphere of radius $r'=40\ \mathrm m$, so

$$ I'=\frac{P}{4\pi r'^2}=I\times\left(\frac{r}{r'}\right)^2=4.0\times10^{-6}\times\left(\frac{10}{40}\right)^2=2.5\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{W/m^2} $$

(the intensity is inversely proportional to the square of the distance). By Formula 11.3, $I\propto A^2$ (the frequency $f$ being the same), so the ratio of the amplitudes is

$$ \frac{A'}{A}=\sqrt{\frac{I'}{I}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{16}}=\frac14 $$

When the distance becomes four times as large, the intensity becomes $1/16$ and the amplitude becomes $1/4$ (the amplitude is inversely proportional to the distance).

← Back to Chapter 11, “Basic Properties of Waves — The Equation of a Wave, Interference, Standing Waves, and Diffraction”

Chapter 12 Sound Waves — The Doppler Effect, Beats, Resonance, and Vibrations of Strings

Exercise 12.1: Calculating the Doppler effect

Solution

From the formula for the speed of sound in Chapter 12, $V=331.5+0.6t$, the speed of sound at an air temperature of $10\,^\circ\mathrm{C}$ is

$$ V=331.5+0.6\times10=331.5+6.0=337.5\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

We use the Doppler effect formula (Formula 12.1 of Chapter 12) $f'=\dfrac{V-v_0}{V-v_s}f$. The observer is at rest, so $v_0=0$ (in both the approaching and the receding case).

(1) When the train approaches the observer, the direction of the source's velocity is the same as the direction in which the sound propagates (from the source toward the observer), so $v_s=+25\ \mathrm{m/s}$.

$$ f'=\frac{337.5-0}{337.5-25}\times600=\frac{337.5}{312.5}\times600\approx648.0\ \mathrm{Hz} $$

(2) When the train passes the observer and moves away, $v_s=-25\ \mathrm{m/s}$.

$$ f''=\frac{337.5-0}{337.5-(-25)}\times600=\frac{337.5}{362.5}\times600\approx558.6\ \mathrm{Hz} $$

Therefore, the observer hears about $648.0\ \mathrm{Hz}$ while the train approaches and about $558.6\ \mathrm{Hz}$ while it recedes.

Exercise 12.2: Finding the frequency difference from the beat period

Solution

The relation between the beat frequency $f_{\text{beat}}$ and the period $T$ (Chapter 12) is $f_{\text{beat}}=1/T=\abs{f_1-f_2}$. Since $T=0.25\ \mathrm s$,

$$ \abs{f_1-f_2}=\frac{1}{T}=\frac{1}{0.25}=4\ \mathrm{Hz} $$

Therefore, the difference between the frequencies of the two tuning forks is $4\ \mathrm{Hz}$. Since one of them is known to be $f_1=392\ \mathrm{Hz}$, the frequency $f_2$ of the other is

$$ f_2=f_1\pm4=392\pm4 $$

That is, either $f_2=396\ \mathrm{Hz}$ or $f_2=388\ \mathrm{Hz}$. The beat frequency (the magnitude of the frequency difference) alone does not determine whether $f_2$ is higher or lower than $f_1$—to decide this, as in Example 12.6, one must change the frequency slightly, for example by adding a small mass to one of the tuning forks, and observe how the beat frequency changes.

Exercise 12.3: Fundamental frequencies of open and closed pipes and the number of resonances

Solution

(1) The natural frequencies of an open pipe are $f_n=nV/(2L)$ and the wavelengths are $\lambda_n=2L/n$ (the open-pipe formulas of Chapter 12; both ends are antinodes, and $L=n\lambda_n/2$). Substituting $L=0.80\ \mathrm m$ and $V=343.5\ \mathrm{m/s}$, the fundamental frequency is

$$ f_1=\frac{V}{2L}=\frac{343.5}{2\times0.80}=\frac{343.5}{1.60}\approx214.7\ \mathrm{Hz} $$

The wavelength and the frequency of the third harmonic ($n=3$) are

$$ \lambda_3=\frac{2L}{3}=\frac{1.60}{3}\approx0.533\ \mathrm m,\qquad f_3=3f_1=\frac{3\times343.5}{1.60}\approx644.1\ \mathrm{Hz} $$

(This also agrees with $f_3=V/\lambda_3=343.5/0.5333\approx644.1\ \mathrm{Hz}$.)

(2) The natural frequencies of a closed pipe of the same length are $f^{(m)}=mV/(4L)$ ($m=1,3,5,\ldots$), so the fundamental frequency is

$$ f^{(1)}=\frac{V}{4L}=\frac{343.5}{4\times0.80}=\frac{343.5}{3.20}\approx107.3\ \mathrm{Hz} $$

(half that of the open pipe). The resonances at or below $20\ \mathrm{kHz}=20000\ \mathrm{Hz}$ are those satisfying $m\times107.34\le20000$, that is, $m\le20000/107.34\approx186.3$; these are the odd numbers $m=1,3,5,\ldots,185$. The number of odd numbers $1,3,\ldots,185$ is $(185+1)/2=93$.

For an open pipe of the same length, $n\times214.69\le20000$ gives $n\le93.2$, that is, $n=1,2,\ldots,93$, which is $93$ values. Therefore, the number of resonances at or below $20\ \mathrm{kHz}$ is the same, $93$, for the closed pipe and the open pipe (the closed pipe has half the fundamental frequency and so reaches lower notes, but only odd multiples appear, so the count comes out exactly the same).

Exercise 12.4: Until the shock wave reaches the ground

Solution

(1) From the formula of Chapter 12, $\sin\theta=V/v_s=1/M$, the half-angle of the Mach cone is

$$ \sin\theta=\frac{1}{1.5}=\frac23\qquad\therefore\ \theta=\arcsin\frac23\approx41.8^\circ $$

(2) The speed of the airplane is $v_s=MV=1.5\times340=510\ \mathrm{m/s}$. When the surface of the Mach cone reaches the observer, the observer lies on a generatrix of the cone, and the airplane (the apex of the cone) has moved horizontally a distance $x$ beyond the point directly above the observer. Since the half-angle of the cone is $\theta$, the straight line joining the apex and the observer (a generatrix) makes the angle $\theta$ with the flight path extended backward, and in terms of the height $h$ we have $\tan\theta=h/x$. Therefore,

$$ x=\frac{h}{\tan\theta},\qquad \tan\theta=\frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta}=\frac{2/3}{\sqrt5/3}=\frac{2}{\sqrt5}\quad\Bigl(\cos\theta=\sqrt{1-\tfrac49}=\tfrac{\sqrt5}{3}\Bigr) $$ $$ x=\frac{8000\sqrt5}{2}=4000\sqrt5\approx8944\ \mathrm m $$

The time the airplane takes to cover this distance is $t_1$, so

$$ t_1=\frac{x}{v_s}=\frac{8944}{510}\approx17.5\ \mathrm s $$

That is, about $17.5\ \mathrm{s}$ after the airplane passes directly overhead, the shock wave (the “boom” sound) arrives.

(3) The engine sound emitted when the airplane was directly overhead travels straight down at the speed of sound $V$ and arrives after $t_2=h/V=8000/340\approx23.5\ \mathrm s$. Therefore, the delay between the arrival of the shock wave and the arrival of the engine sound is

$$ t_2-t_1\approx23.53-17.54\approx6.0\ \mathrm s $$

In other words, the engine sound is heard this much later than the shock wave. In general, $t_2-t_1=\dfrac hV\left(1-\dfrac{\sqrt{M^2-1}}{M}\right)$ (we used $\cot\theta=\sqrt{M^2-1}$).

Exercise 12.5: A source and an observer moving obliquely

Solution

We use the angle form of Formula 12.2 (Chapter 12), $f'=f\,\dfrac{V-v_{\mathrm O}\cos\theta_{\mathrm O}}{V-v_{\mathrm S}\cos\theta_{\mathrm S}}$. Here $\theta_{\mathrm S},\theta_{\mathrm O}$ are the angles between the direction of the sound ray (from the source toward the observer) and the velocities of the source and of the observer, respectively.

(1) The observer is at rest ($v_{\mathrm O}=0$), and the source has $v_{\mathrm S}=20\ \mathrm{m/s}$ and $\theta_{\mathrm S}=60^\circ$, so $v_{\mathrm S}\cos\theta_{\mathrm S}=20\times\tfrac12=10\ \mathrm{m/s}$.

$$ f'=\frac{340}{340-10}\times1000=\frac{340}{330}\times1000\approx1030\ \mathrm{Hz} $$

(The component of the source's velocity along the line of sight is $+10\ \mathrm{m/s}$; since the source has a component in the direction of approach, the sound is heard higher than the frequency actually emitted.)

(2) The source is at rest ($v_{\mathrm S}=0$), and the observer has $v_{\mathrm O}=10\ \mathrm{m/s}$ and $\theta_{\mathrm O}=120^\circ$, so $v_{\mathrm O}\cos\theta_{\mathrm O}=10\times(-\tfrac12)=-5\ \mathrm{m/s}$ (a component toward the source).

$$ f'=\frac{340-(-5)}{340}\times1000=\frac{345}{340}\times1000\approx1015\ \mathrm{Hz} $$

(Since the observer has a component toward the source, the sound is heard higher than the frequency actually emitted.)

Exercise 12.6: Finding the speed of sound and the end correction from a resonance-tube experiment

Solution

(1) In the resonance of a closed pipe, the effective length $l+\Delta\ell$ (from the water surface, which is the closed end and a node, to the antinode at the open end) is an odd multiple of $\lambda/4$. For the first resonance (fundamental mode) and the next resonance (third harmonic),

$$ l_1+\Delta\ell=\frac{\lambda}{4},\qquad l_2+\Delta\ell=\frac{3\lambda}{4} $$

Subtracting these two equations eliminates the unknown $\Delta\ell$, and we obtain

$$ l_2-l_1=\frac{\lambda}{2}\qquad\therefore\ \lambda=2(l_2-l_1)=2\times(0.510-0.160)=0.700\ \mathrm m $$

The speed of sound is $V=f\lambda=500\times0.700=350\ \mathrm{m/s}$.

(2) From $l_1+\Delta\ell=\lambda/4$,

$$ \Delta\ell=\frac{\lambda}{4}-l_1=0.175-0.160=0.015\ \mathrm m=1.5\ \mathrm{cm} $$

(This also agrees with $\Delta\ell=0.525-0.510=0.015\ \mathrm m$ obtained from the other equation $l_2+\Delta\ell=3\lambda/4$. Also, using $\Delta\ell\approx0.6r_0$, the radius of the tube is estimated to be $r_0\approx2.5\ \mathrm{cm}$.)

(3) From Definition 12.1 (the formula $V=331.5+0.6t$),

$$ t=\frac{V-331.5}{0.6}=\frac{350-331.5}{0.6}\approx30.8\,^\circ\mathrm{C} $$

That is, the air temperature is about $31\,^\circ\mathrm{C}$.

Exercise 12.7: Tension of a string and higher modes of vibration

Solution

(1) Solving the formula for the speed of a wave on a string (Chapter 12), $v=\sqrt{S/\rho}$, for the tension $S$ gives $S=\rho v^2$.

$$ S=\rho v^2=1.0\times10^{-3}\times250^2=1.0\times10^{-3}\times6.25\times10^4=62.5\ \mathrm{N} $$

(2) Substituting $L=0.60\ \mathrm m$ and $v=250\ \mathrm{m/s}$ into the formula for the natural frequencies of a string fixed at both ends (Formula 12.3 of Chapter 12), $f_n=nv/(2L)$, we obtain

$$ f_1=\frac{v}{2L}=\frac{250}{1.20}\approx208.3\ \mathrm{Hz},\qquad f_2=2f_1\approx416.7\ \mathrm{Hz},\qquad f_3=3f_1=625\ \mathrm{Hz} $$

(3) The displacement of the third harmonic is $y\propto\sin(3\pi x/L)$ ($\lambda_3=2L/3$, $k_3=3\pi/L$). The antinodes are the positions where $\abs{\sin(3\pi x/L)}=1$, that is, $3\pi x/L=\pi/2,\ 3\pi/2,\ 5\pi/2$, so

$$ x=\frac{L}{6},\ \frac{L}{2},\ \frac{5L}{6}=0.10\ \mathrm m,\ 0.30\ \mathrm m,\ 0.50\ \mathrm m $$

(The nodes are at $x=0,\ L/3,\ 2L/3,\ L$, that is, $0,\ 0.20,\ 0.40,\ 0.60\ \mathrm m$, and the antinodes lie midway between them.)

Exercise 12.8: Why does the frequency double in the axial arrangement of a tuning fork?

Solution

If the tuning fork is mounted axially (the direction in which its arms open and close is parallel to the string), the arm merely moves one end of the string back and forth along the string and does not directly shake the string sideways. Instead, when the arm moves in the direction that pulls on the string (outward), the string is pulled taut and the tension increases, and when the arm moves inward, the string slackens and the tension decreases.
