During one back-and-forth motion of the tuning-fork arm (open → close → open), the tension reaches its maximum only once, so the tension varies periodically at the same frequency $f_{\text{fork}}$ as the tuning fork.

Next, consider the transverse vibration of the string. When the string undergoes a small transverse vibration $y(x,t)$, its total length increases from $L$ by

$$ \Delta\ell_{\text{elongation}}\approx\frac12\int_0^L\left(\pdiff{y}{x}\right)^2\dd x $$

This is proportional to the square of the slope $\partial y/\partial x$, that is, to the square of the magnitude of the displacement, and it takes the same value whether the displacement is positive (a crest) or negative (a trough). For example, for $y=a\sin(2\pi x/L)\cos\omega_{\text{string}}t$ (a standing wave of one wavelength), the elongation is $\dfrac{\pi^2a^2}{L}\cos^2\omega_{\text{string}}t$, which is largest when the displacement is largest (crest or trough) and zero when the string is straight ($y=0$). The more the string stretches, the larger the tension becomes, so the tension of the string varies as follows: large when the string is at a crest, small when it is straight, large when it is at a trough, small when it is straight, and so on. During one vibration of the string, the tension reaches its maximum twice (at the crest and at the trough).

It is only when the tuning fork vibrates exactly twice (so that the tension maximum occurs twice) during one vibration of the string (during which the tension maximum occurs twice) that the times at which the tuning fork changes the tension of the string match the times at which the motion of the string requires the tension to change. Then the tension reaches its maximum each time the string is at a crest or a trough, energy is continually supplied to the vibration of the string, and the string resonates and vibrates with a large amplitude. Therefore,

$$ f_{\text{fork}}=2f_{\text{string}} $$

holds. In the horizontal arrangement, on the other hand, the arm shakes the string directly sideways, so one vibration of the tuning fork corresponds to one transverse vibration of the string, and $f_{\text{fork}}=f_{\text{string}}$. (This is the mechanism of parametric excitation, the same as in pumping a swing: the swing grows larger if you stand up and crouch twice during one back-and-forth swing.)

← Back to Chapter 12, “Sound Waves — The Doppler Effect, Beats, Resonance, and Vibrations of Strings”

Chapter 13 Fundamentals of Light Waves — Measuring the Speed of Light and the Refractive Index

Exercise 13.1: Intensity of light passing through polarizers

Solution

When unpolarized (natural) light passes through the first polarizer, vibrations in all directions are averaged and the intensity is halved:

$$ I_1=\frac{I_0}{2} $$

The transmission axis of the second polarizer is tilted by $60^{\circ}$ from the direction of vibration just after passing through the first, so applying Malus's law $I=I_0\cos^2\theta$ gives

$$ I_2=I_1\cos^2 60^{\circ}=\frac{I_0}{2}\times\left(\frac12\right)^2=\frac{I_0}{2}\times\frac14=\frac{I_0}{8} $$

Therefore, the intensity of the light after passing through the second polarizer is $I_2=\dfrac{I_0}{8}$ ($12.5\ \%$ of the incident unpolarized light).

Exercise 13.2: Inserting a third polarizer between crossed Nicols

Solution

Let the transmission axis of the first polarizer be at angle $0^{\circ}$ and that of the second polarizer, which is in the crossed-Nicols arrangement, at angle $90^{\circ}$, and insert between them a third polarizer at angle $45^{\circ}$ (making $45^{\circ}$ with each of the two). Let the intensity of the unpolarized light be $I_0$. At each polarizer, we apply Malus's law, taking the direction of the transmission axis of the preceding polarizer as the new “direction of vibration.”

Immediately after the first polarizer (which transmits the unpolarized light):

$$ I_a=\frac{I_0}{2} $$

Immediately after the third polarizer (angle $45^{\circ}$, an angle difference of $45^{\circ}$ from the first):

$$ I_b=I_a\cos^2 45^{\circ}=\frac{I_0}{2}\times\frac12=\frac{I_0}{4} $$

Immediately after the second polarizer (angle $90^{\circ}$, an angle difference of $45^{\circ}$ from the third):

$$ I_c=I_b\cos^2 45^{\circ}=\frac{I_0}{4}\times\frac12=\frac{I_0}{8} $$

Therefore, the intensity of the light finally transmitted is $I_c=\dfrac{I_0}{8}$ ($12.5\ \%$).

Before the third polarizer is inserted, the first (angle $0^{\circ}$) and the second (angle $90^{\circ}$) are directly in a crossed-Nicols relationship, so $I=I_0\cos^2 90^{\circ}=0$ and no light passes through at all. Yet when the third polarizer at angle $45^{\circ}$ is inserted between them, light of intensity $I_0/8$ passes through after all—this is because a polarizer is not merely a device that “absorbs and weakens light” but a device that projects the vibration of the light onto the direction of the transmission axis and extracts only that component. After passing through the first polarizer, the light is linearly polarized in the $0^{\circ}$ direction. After passing through the third ($45^{\circ}$), its amplitude is multiplied by $\cos45^{\circ}$ and it is linearly polarized in the $45^{\circ}$ direction. This light again has a $45^{\circ}$ component with respect to the transmission axis of the second polarizer ($90^{\circ}$), so it can pass through. It is a seemingly counterintuitive result: the polarizers do not “rotate” the direction of vibration; rather, the extraction of components is split into two steps, $0^{\circ}\to45^{\circ}\to90^{\circ}$, and that is why the light gets through.

Exercise 13.3: Differences between the properties of sound and light

Solution

(1) Presence or absence of dispersion. The speed at which a wave propagates through a medium is determined by what kind of restoring force the medium exerts against displacement (its elasticity). For sound waves and light waves, the mechanism by which this restoring force arises is entirely different.

Sound waves (why there is almost no dispersion in the audible range). The restoring force for a sound wave in a gas originates from the macroscopic elasticity of pressure (the compressibility) exhibited by the collective of gas molecules. The speed of sound is essentially of the form $V=\sqrt{\gamma RT/M}$ ($\gamma$: heat capacity ratio, $M$: molar mass, $T$: absolute temperature), which is determined solely by the macroscopic properties of the gas, namely its temperature and composition, and the frequency of the sound appears nowhere in this expression. The periods in the audible range (roughly $20\ \mathrm{Hz}\sim20\ \mathrm{kHz}$) are far longer than the time scale over which collisions between gas molecules produce this macroscopic elasticity, so the gas responds to a sound wave as “a continuum that behaves elastically in the same way regardless of frequency.” This is why sound in the audible range shows almost no dispersion.

Light waves (why they show dispersion in transparent substances). When light (an electromagnetic wave) propagates through a transparent substance, the restoring force of the substance originates from the forced oscillation, driven by the electric field of the light, of the individual electrons bound to atoms and molecules (they are shaken like small spring pendulums with natural angular frequency $\omega_0$). Because the response of the forced oscillation of an electron depends on the relation between the driving angular frequency $\omega$ (= the frequency of the light) and the natural angular frequency $\omega_0$, specifically on the form $1/(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)$, the refractive index $n(\omega)$ takes a different value at each frequency (wavelength)—this is dispersion.

In short, the difference is this: the restoring force of a sound wave originates from a macroscopic elasticity that does not depend on frequency (a property of the medium as a whole), so almost no dispersion occurs, whereas the restoring force of a light wave originates from the resonant forced oscillation of individual electrons that respond sensitively to frequency, so dispersion inherently occurs.

(2) Polarization and a summary of the properties. Polarization means that the direction of vibration of a transverse wave is restricted to a single direction in the plane perpendicular to the direction of propagation. The direction of vibration of a transverse wave can take various directions in the plane perpendicular to the direction of propagation (there are two degrees of freedom), so a distinction arises between selecting and not selecting that direction. In contrast, for a sound wave in a gas, which is a longitudinal wave, the direction of vibration is always fixed on the single straight line along the direction of propagation, and since there is only one direction to choose from, the distinction of a “directional bias” does not exist. That light exhibits polarization is evidence that light is a transverse wave.

The differences in properties are summarized in the following table.

ItemLightSound (in gases and liquids)
Type of waveTransverse wave (oscillation of the electric and magnetic fields) → polarization existsLongitudinal wave (oscillation of density and pressure) → no polarization
What determines the speedThe medium and the wavelength (constant at $c_0$ in vacuum)The medium and the temperature
DispersionYes (colors are separated by a prism)Almost none in the audible range
Order of magnitude of the wavelength$10^{-7}\sim10^{-6}\ \mathrm{m}$ → diffraction is inconspicuous$10^{-2}\sim10\ \mathrm{m}$ → diffraction is pronounced

Exercise 13.4: Time for light to travel the Earth–Sun distance

Solution

Using the defined value of the speed of light in vacuum, $c_0=299\,792\,458\ \mathrm{m/s}$, and the defined value of the astronomical unit, $1\ \mathrm{au}=1.495\,978\,707\times10^{11}\ \mathrm{m}$, we have

$$ \Delta t=\frac{1\ \mathrm{au}}{c_0}=\frac{1.495\,978\,707\times10^{11}\ \mathrm{m}}{2.997\,924\,58\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s}}\approx499.0\ \mathrm{s}\approx8.32\ \mathrm{min} $$

This is exactly half of “the time for light to cross the diameter of Earth's orbit ($2\ \mathrm{au}$)” (about $998.0\ \mathrm{s}\approx16.6$ minutes) found in Example 13.5 of Chapter 13. This is of course the expected result—the diameter of the orbit is exactly twice the distance of the radius ($1\ \mathrm{au}$), and since the speed of light is constant, the time required to travel it is exactly twice as well.

Exercise 13.5: Fizeau's experiment (different data)

Solution

The light “first became completely invisible” when the gear wheel turned by exactly half a pitch during the round trip. Therefore, substituting $n=25.0\ \mathrm{/s}$, $N=600$, and $L=5.00\times10^3\ \mathrm{m}$ into Formula 13.1 of Chapter 13 (the formula for the speed of light from Fizeau's experiment), $c_0=4nNL$, we obtain

$$ c_0=4\times25.0\ \mathrm{/s}\times600\times5.00\times10^3\ \mathrm{m}=3.00\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

This is a value very close to the defined value of the speed of light in vacuum, $c_0=2.997\,924\,58\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s}$.

Check: the round-trip time is $2L/c_0=2\times5.00\times10^3/(3.00\times10^8)\approx3.33\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{s}$, and the time for the half-pitch rotation is $1/(2nN)=1/(2\times25.0\times600)\approx3.33\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{s}$; the two agree.

The light next appears brightest when the gear wheel turns exactly one pitch during the round trip; the condition is $2L/c_0=1/(n'N)$, that is, $c_0=2n'NL$. Therefore

$$ n'=\frac{c_0}{2NL}=\frac{3.00\times10^8}{2\times600\times5.00\times10^3}\ \mathrm{/s}=50.0\ \mathrm{/s} $$

which is exactly twice the rotation rate at which the light first disappeared ($25.0\ \mathrm{/s}$).

Exercise 13.6: The speed of light in diamond

Solution

Solving the definition of the absolute refractive index, $n=c_0/v$, for $v$ gives $v=c_0/n$. Substituting $n\approx2.42$ gives

$$ v=\frac{c_0}{n}=\frac{2.997\,924\,58\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s}}{2.42}\approx1.239\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

The time to pass through diamond of thickness $d=1.0\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{m}$ is, from $t=d/v$,

$$ t=\frac{1.0\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{m}}{1.239\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s}}\approx8.07\times10^{-11}\ \mathrm{s}=80.7\ \mathrm{ps} $$

Comparing this with the time it takes light to travel the same $1.0\ \mathrm{cm}$ in vacuum ($d/c_0\approx33.4\ \mathrm{ps}$), we see that in diamond, which has a large refractive index, light takes exactly $n\approx2.42$ times as long (this is natural since, from $v=c_0/n$, we have $t=d/v=nd/c_0$).

Exercise 13.7: Calculating the speed of light by the rotating-mirror method

Solution

(1) The time for light to make a round trip between the rotating mirror and the concave mirror is $t=2R/c_0$, and the angle through which the mirror turns during this time is $\theta=\omega t=2\pi n\times2R/c_0=4\pi nR/c_0$. Using the approximate value $c_0\approx2.998\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s}$ to estimate $\theta$, we get

$$ \theta=\frac{4\pi\times400.0\times10.0}{2.998\times10^8}\ \mathrm{rad}=\frac{5.027\times10^4}{2.998\times10^8}\ \mathrm{rad}\approx1.68\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{rad} $$

and the deviation in the direction of the returning light is $2\theta\approx3.35\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{rad}$. From $\tan x=x+x^3/3+\cdots$, the relative error of the approximation $\tan2\theta\approx2\theta$ is $(2\theta)^2/3\approx4\times10^{-8}$, so the approximation is quite good.

(2) Substitute $\ell=1.006\ \mathrm{cm}=1.006\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{m}$ into Formula 13.2, $c_0=8\pi nRL/\ell$. The numerator is $8\pi\times400.0\times10.0\times30.0=3.016\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{m^2/s}$, so

$$ c_0=\frac{3.016\times10^{6}}{1.006\times10^{-2}}\ \mathrm{m/s}\approx3.00\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

(Check: $\ell=L\times2\theta=30.0\times3.35\times10^{-4}\approx1.006\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{m}$.)

(3) Since $\ell=8\pi nRL/c_0$ is proportional to $n$, doubling the rotation rate to $800.0\ \mathrm{/s}$ doubles the deviation to $\ell\approx2.01\ \mathrm{cm}$. The higher the rotation rate, the larger the deviation and the easier it is to measure (Foucault's experiment also used a rapidly rotating mirror).

Exercise 13.8: Refinements of Fizeau's experiment

Solution

(1) $n=\dfrac{c_0}{4NL}=\dfrac{2.998\times10^8}{4\times720\times100}\ \mathrm{/s}\approx1.04\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{/s}$; that is, about 1040 revolutions per second (about 60,000 revolutions per minute) are required. Spinning a gear wheel with 720 teeth at this speed is unrealistic with the technology of the nineteenth century (and, for a large gear wheel, even today). In addition, the round-trip time $2L/c_0=2\times100/(2.998\times10^8)\approx6.7\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{s}$ is too short, so it is difficult to achieve a rotation so fast that the gear wheel turns half a pitch in that interval and to measure the rotation rate accurately.

(2) To make $n=c_0/(4NL)$ smaller, it suffices to make the product $NL$ larger. Either increase the number of teeth $N$ (make the teeth finer) or lengthen the distance $L$ to the mirror, or do both. For example, to make $n=12.6\ \mathrm{/s}$ or so requires $NL=c_0/(4n)\approx5.9\times10^6\ \mathrm{m}$, and with $N=720$ this gives $L\approx8.3\ \mathrm{km}$ (almost the same as in Fizeau's experiment). However, if $L$ is made too long, the light making the round trip spreads out and weakens, making it difficult to judge whether the light has disappeared.

(3) In $c_0=4nNL$, $N$ is an integer and has no error. If the errors in $n$ and $L$ are independent, the relative errors add, and

$$ \frac{\Delta c_0}{c_0}\approx\frac{\Delta n}{n}+\frac{\Delta L}{L} $$

(the relative error of a quantity in product form can be estimated as the sum of the relative errors of the factors). For example, if $\Delta n/n=1\ \%$ and $\Delta L/L=0.1\ \%$, then $\Delta c_0/c_0\approx1.1\ \%$, and most of the error comes from the measurement of the rotation rate and from how the rotation rate at which the light is judged to have “disappeared” is determined (since the light darkens continuously toward extinction, there is a spread in the judgment). Therefore, to measure the speed of light with good precision, it is important to improve the precision of the rotation-rate measurement and of the judgment of extinction (or of maximum brightness).

← Back to Chapter 13, “Fundamentals of Light Waves — Measuring the Speed of Light and the Refractive Index”

Chapter 14 Reflection and Refraction of Light — Phase Changes, Fermat's Principle, and Total Internal Reflection

Exercise 14.1: Reflection at a fixed end of an incident wave with a general initial phase

Solution

Let the incident wave be $y_i=A\sin(\omega t-kx+\delta)$ and the reflected wave be $y_r=A\sin(\omega t+kx+\delta+\phi)$ ($\phi$ is the phase shift relative to $y_i$). Substituting $x=0$ into the fixed-end boundary condition $(y_i+y_r)|_{x=0}=0$ gives

$$ A\sin(\omega t+\delta) + A\sin(\omega t+\delta+\phi) = 0 $$

Now let $\theta\equiv\omega t+\delta$ (since $\delta$ is a constant, $\theta$ ranges over all real numbers when $t$ does). Then this equation can be rewritten as

$$ A\sin\theta + A\sin(\theta+\phi) = 0 $$

This has exactly the same form as the equation treated in the main text of Chapter 14 with $\theta=\omega t$. Using the sum-to-product formula $\sin\alpha+\sin\beta=2\sin\frac{\alpha+\beta}2\cos\frac{\alpha-\beta}2$, we obtain

$$ A\sin\theta+A\sin(\theta+\phi)=2A\sin\left(\theta+\frac\phi2\right)\cos\frac\phi2=0 $$

Since $A\neq0$, we have $\sin(\theta+\phi/2)\cos(\phi/2)=0$. This equation must hold for every value of $\theta$ (and hence of $t$). If $\cos(\phi/2)\neq0$, it would require $\sin(\theta+\phi/2)=0$ for every $\theta$; but $\sin$ is a function whose value changes if $\theta$ is moved even slightly, so this cannot hold. Therefore

$$ \cos\frac\phi2=0 \qquad\therefore\ \frac\phi2=\left(n+\frac12\right)\pi\ (n\in\mathbb Z) \qquad\therefore\ \phi=(2n+1)\pi $$

In the range $0\leqq\phi\lt2\pi$, only $\phi=\pi$ (with $n=0$) satisfies the condition.

That is, no matter what initial phase $\delta$ is given to the incident wave, the relative phase shift at fixed-end reflection is exactly the same as in the main text, $\phi=\pi$. This is also a natural result—$\delta$ merely changes “where to place the origin of time,” and the physical phenomenon of reflection itself (how energy is thrown back at the boundary) should not depend on the choice of the time origin.

Exercise 14.2: Amplitudes of the reflected and transmitted waves when the linear densities are reversed

Solution

The speed of a wave on a string is $v=\sqrt{S/\rho}$, so

$$ v_1=\sqrt{\frac{10}{1.0}}=3.162\ \mathrm{m/s},\qquad v_2=\sqrt{\frac{10}{4.0}}=1.581\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

The angular frequency is common to both strings, $\omega=2\pi f=2\pi\ \mathrm{rad/s}$, and the wavenumber is $k=\omega/v$, so

$$ k_1=\frac{2\pi}{v_1}=1.987\ \mathrm{rad/m},\qquad k_2=\frac{2\pi}{v_2}=3.974\ \mathrm{rad/m} $$

Since $\rho_1=1.0\ \mathrm{kg/m}\lt\rho_2=4.0\ \mathrm{kg/m}$, Case [3] of Section 14.2 ($(\alpha,\beta)=(0,\pi)$, $\rho_1\lt\rho_2$) applies. The formulas for Case [3] are

$$ B=\frac{2k_1}{k_1+k_2}A,\qquad C=\frac{k_2-k_1}{k_1+k_2}A $$

Here $k_2/k_1=v_1/v_2=\sqrt{\rho_2/\rho_1}=\sqrt{4.0}=2.0$, so $k_2=2k_1$, a simple ratio. Substituting this gives

$$ B=\frac{2k_1}{k_1+2k_1}A=\frac23A=\frac23\times2.0=1.33\ \mathrm{cm} $$ $$ C=\frac{2k_1-k_1}{k_1+2k_1}A=\frac13A=\frac13\times2.0=0.67\ \mathrm{cm} $$

In Case [3] the phase shift of the reflected wave is $\beta=\pi$, which has the same form as the fixed-end reflection of Section 14.1 of the main text ($\phi=\pi$), so this reflection is fixed-end-like (this agrees with the intuition that a wave going from the light string $\rho_1$ to the heavy string $\rho_2$ behaves much like the fixed-end reflection of a wave “hitting a wall”).

Exercise 14.3: Angle of refraction at a water surface

Solution

Solving the law of refraction of Chapter 14, $\sin\theta_1/\sin\theta_2=n_2/n_1$, for $\sin\theta_2$ gives

$$ \sin\theta_2=\frac{n_1}{n_2}\sin\theta_1=\frac{1.0}{1.33}\times\sin40^\circ=\frac{1.0}{1.33}\times0.6428=0.4833 $$ $$ \theta_2=\arcsin(0.4833)\fallingdotseq28.9^\circ $$

Since $n_2=1.33\gt n_1=1.0$ (the light goes from the smaller absolute refractive index to the larger), we have $\theta_2\lt\theta_1$ ($28.9^\circ\lt40^\circ$), and the refracted ray bends toward the normal. This is the same as the rule “on entering a denser medium, the ray bends toward the normal” confirmed in Example 14.4.

Exercise 14.4: Computing the angle of refraction by Fermat's principle (from water to air)

Solution

The optical path length is $L(x)=n_1\sqrt{x^2+h_1^2}+n_2\sqrt{(d-x)^2+h_2^2}$ ($n_1=1.33,\ h_1=4.0,\ n_2=1.0,\ h_2=1.0,\ d=6.0$). Differentiating by exactly the same procedure as in the derivation in Chapter 14 gives

$$ L'(x)=\frac{n_1x}{\sqrt{x^2+h_1^2}}-\frac{n_2(d-x)}{\sqrt{(d-x)^2+h_2^2}}=n_1\sin\theta_1-n_2\sin\theta_2 $$

Set $L'(x)=0$ and solve the resulting equation

$$ \frac{1.33\,x}{\sqrt{x^2+16.0}}=\frac{1.0\,(6.0-x)}{\sqrt{(6.0-x)^2+1.0}} $$

Squaring both sides gives a quartic equation, which is tedious to solve by hand, so instead we examine the sign change of $f(x)\equiv L'(x)$ and bracket the root. For example, at $x=3.7$,

$$ f(3.7)=\frac{1.33\times3.7}{\sqrt{3.7^2+16.0}}-\frac{2.3}{\sqrt{2.3^2+1.0}}=\frac{4.921}{5.4489}-\frac{2.3}{2.5080}=0.9031-0.9171=-0.0139 $$

and at $x=3.8$,

$$ f(3.8)=\frac{1.33\times3.8}{\sqrt{3.8^2+16.0}}-\frac{2.2}{\sqrt{2.2^2+1.0}}=\frac{5.054}{5.5172}-\frac{2.2}{2.4166}=0.9160-0.9104=+0.0057 $$
$x\ /\ \mathrm{m}$$0$$3.0$$3.7$$3.8$$6.0$
$f(x)$$-0.986$$-0.151$$-0.014$$+0.006$$+1.107$

Since $f(3.7)\lt0$ and $f(3.8)\gt0$, the sign changes, so $3.7\lt x_0\lt3.8$. Treating $f$ as a straight line in this interval and interpolating linearly, we obtain

$$ x_0\fallingdotseq3.8-0.0057\times\frac{0.1}{0.0057+0.0139}=3.8-0.029\fallingdotseq3.77\ \mathrm{m} $$

At this point,

$$ \theta_1=\arctan\frac{x_0}{h_1}=\arctan\frac{3.77}{4.0}\fallingdotseq43.3^\circ,\qquad \theta_2=\arctan\frac{d-x_0}{h_2}=\arctan\frac{2.23}{1.0}\fallingdotseq65.8^\circ $$

and therefore

$$ n_1\sin\theta_1=1.33\times\sin43.3^\circ\fallingdotseq0.912,\qquad n_2\sin\theta_2=1.0\times\sin65.8^\circ\fallingdotseq0.912 $$

The two agree, so we confirm that Snell's law is satisfied. Moreover, $L(3.0)=9.812\ \mathrm{m}$, $L(3.77)=9.754\ \mathrm{m}$, and $L(4.0)=9.760\ \mathrm{m}$, so $L$ is indeed smallest at $x_0$. Since $n_1=1.33\gt n_2=1.0$ (the light goes from the denser to the less dense medium), we have $\theta_2\gt\theta_1$, which shows that the refracted ray bends away from the normal (this is exactly the configuration in which the total internal reflection studied right afterward in Section 14.7 can occur).

Exercise 14.5: Critical angle of diamond

Solution

Substituting $n_1=2.42$ (diamond) and $n_2=1.0$ (air) into the definition of the critical angle, $\sin\theta_c=n_2/n_1$, gives

$$ \sin\theta_c=\frac{1.0}{2.42}=0.413 $$ $$ \theta_c=\arcsin(0.413)\fallingdotseq24.4^\circ $$

This is considerably smaller than the critical angle of glass (Example 14.9, $\theta_c\fallingdotseq41.8^\circ$). A small critical angle means that light traveling even slightly obliquely inside the diamond undergoes total internal reflection unless it strikes the boundary at an angle close to perpendicular, so inside a cut diamond much of the incident light is totally internally reflected many times before it emerges. These “many total internal reflections” are one reason a diamond appears to sparkle so strongly (the cutting techniques of gem polishing design the angles so that total internal reflection occurs effectively).

Exercise 14.6: The principle of the optical fiber (short essay and numerical calculation)

Solution

(1) The core of an optical fiber is made with a larger absolute refractive index than the surrounding cladding. When light that has entered the core strikes the core–cladding boundary at an angle equal to or greater than the critical angle, total internal reflection occurs, and the light is reflected and keeps traveling without leaving the core. Because this is repeated many times inside the fiber, the light can travel long distances with almost no loss.

(2) The critical angle follows from $\sin\theta_c=n_2/n_1$:

$$ \sin\theta_c=\frac{1.44}{1.48}=0.973 \qquad\therefore\ \theta_c\fallingdotseq76.6^\circ $$

The numerical aperture is

$$ \mathrm{NA}=\sqrt{n_1^2-n_2^2}=\sqrt{1.48^2-1.44^2}=\sqrt{2.1904-2.0736}=\sqrt{0.1168}\fallingdotseq0.342 $$

Compared with Example 14.10 (core $1.50$, cladding $1.46$, $\theta_c\fallingdotseq76.7^\circ$, $\mathrm{NA}\fallingdotseq0.344$), the difference between core and cladding is the same in both fibers, $0.04$. However, since the refractive index values themselves are slightly smaller for this fiber, the ratio $n_2/n_1$ is slightly smaller, $0.9730$ (versus $0.9733$ in Example 14.10), and the critical angle is also slightly smaller, $76.6^\circ$ (versus $76.7^\circ$ in Example 14.10). The numerical aperture is likewise slightly smaller, $0.342$ versus $0.344$, because $n_1+n_2$ in $\mathrm{NA}=\sqrt{(n_1-n_2)(n_1+n_2)}$ is smaller, and the range of angles over which light can be accepted is correspondingly very slightly narrower.

Exercise 14.7: Arranging the electromagnetic spectrum in order of wavelength

Solution

As confirmed in Figure 14.8, electromagnetic waves are called by different names depending on their wavelength, but they are essentially the same kind of wave. The order from longer to shorter wavelength is

radio waves > infrared > visible light > ultraviolet > X-rays > $\gamma$-rays

Since frequency is inversely proportional to wavelength ($f=c_0/\lambda$, where $c_0$ is the speed of light in vacuum, a constant), this order coincides exactly with the order from lower to higher frequency—that is, $\gamma$-rays have the highest frequency (the highest energy), and radio waves have the lowest frequency (the lowest energy).

Within visible light, red ($\lambda_1\fallingdotseq7.7\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm m$) has a longer wavelength than blue-violet ($\lambda_2\fallingdotseq3.8\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm m$). Therefore, even within visible light, red has the lower frequency and blue-violet the higher frequency (as calculated in Example 14.7, $f_1\fallingdotseq3.89\times10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}\lt f_2\fallingdotseq7.89\times10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}$).

Exercise 14.8: Phase shift in reflection at a free end (general initial phase)

Solution

Taking the partial derivative of $y_i+y_r$ with respect to $x$ gives

$$ \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(y_i+y_r)=-kA\cos(\omega t-kx+\delta)+kA\cos(\omega t+kx+\delta+\phi) $$

When we substitute $x=0$ and let $\theta\equiv\omega t+\delta$, the boundary condition becomes

$$ -kA\cos\theta+kA\cos(\theta+\phi)=0 $$

Using the sum-to-product formula $\cos\alpha-\cos\beta=-2\sin\dfrac{\alpha+\beta}{2}\sin\dfrac{\alpha-\beta}{2}$ ($\alpha=\theta+\phi,\ \beta=\theta$), we obtain

$$ kA\left[\cos(\theta+\phi)-\cos\theta\right]=-2kA\sin\left(\theta+\frac\phi2\right)\sin\frac\phi2=0 $$

Since $kA\neq0$, this equation must hold for every $\theta$ (and hence at all times $t$). If $\sin(\phi/2)\neq0$, then $\sin(\theta+\phi/2)=0$ would have to hold for every $\theta$, which is impossible. Therefore $\sin(\phi/2)=0$, that is, $\phi/2=n\pi$ ($n$ an integer), so $\phi=2n\pi$. In the range $0\leqq\phi\lt2\pi$, only $\phi=0$ (with $n=0$) satisfies the condition.

The initial phase $\delta$ does not appear in this result. Since the initial phase $\delta$ merely changes the choice of the time origin, the phase shift at free-end reflection is $\phi=0$, the same as in the main text, regardless of $\delta$.

Exercise 14.9: Drawing a light path using the laws of reflection and refraction

Solution

(1) By the law of reflection (Law 14.1), the angle of reflection equals the angle of incidence, $50^\circ$. The angle of refraction follows from the law of refraction (Law 14.2), $\sin\theta_2=\dfrac{n_1}{n_2}\sin\theta_1$:

$$ \sin\theta_2=\frac{1.00}{1.33}\times\sin50^\circ=\frac{0.7660}{1.33}=0.5760\qquad\therefore\ \theta_2=\arcsin0.5760\fallingdotseq35.2^\circ $$

Since $n_2\gt n_1$, we have $\theta_2\lt\theta_1$ ($35.2^\circ\lt50^\circ$), and the refracted ray bends toward the normal. The construction is done as follows.

  1. Draw a horizontal line as the boundary, with air above and water below.
  2. Take a point $\mathrm{O}$ on the boundary and draw the vertical normal (dashed line) through $\mathrm{O}$, extending both above and below.
  3. On the upper side, draw the incident ray, with an arrow, heading toward $\mathrm{O}$ from the direction $50^\circ$ to the left of the normal.
  4. On the upper side, draw the reflected ray leaving $\mathrm{O}$ in the direction $50^\circ$ to the right of the normal (symmetric to the incident ray about the normal).
  5. On the lower side, draw the refracted ray leaving $\mathrm{O}$ in the direction $35.2^\circ$ to the right of the normal (closer to the normal than the extension of the incident ray).

(2) For incidence from water into air, $n_1=1.33,\ n_2=1.00$ and $\theta_1=50^\circ$, so the law of refraction gives

$$ \sin\theta_2=\frac{n_1}{n_2}\sin\theta_1=\frac{1.33}{1.00}\times0.7660=1.019\gt1 $$

Since $\sin\theta_2$ exceeds $1$, no angle of refraction $\theta_2$ satisfies this equation. This corresponds to the fact that the angle of incidence $50^\circ$ is larger than the critical angle $\theta_c=\arcsin(1.00/1.33)\fallingdotseq48.8^\circ$. Therefore no refracted light is produced, and all of the light is reflected at the boundary (total internal reflection). The angle of reflection is, as before, equal to the angle of incidence, $50^\circ$.

Exercise 14.10: Why a prism bends violet light more than red light

Solution

(1) When light goes from air ($n_1\fallingdotseq1$) into glass ($n_2$), the law of refraction becomes $\sin\theta_2=\dfrac{1}{n_2}\sin\theta_1$. For the same angle of incidence $\theta_1$, the larger $n_2$ is, the smaller $\sin\theta_2$ is, and the smaller the angle of refraction $\theta_2$ is (the ray bends toward the normal to a greater degree, and is deflected more from its incident direction). In glass, “the shorter the wavelength, the larger the absolute refractive index,” so blue-violet light, with its shorter wavelength, has a larger $n_2$ than red light and a smaller $\theta_2$—that is, it is bent more. In a prism, the light bends in the same direction at both the entrance face and the exit face (toward the side opposite the apex angle, that is, the base side), so the color-dependent difference in bending accumulates over the two refractions and grows larger, and the colors separate clearly.

(2) Substitute $\sin60^\circ=0.8660$ into the law of refraction $\sin\theta_2=\sin60^\circ/n$.

$$ \sin\theta_{2,\text{red}}=\frac{0.8660}{1.514}=0.5720\ \Longrightarrow\ \theta_{2,\text{red}}\fallingdotseq34.89^\circ $$ $$ \sin\theta_{2,\text{blue}}=\frac{0.8660}{1.522}=0.5690\ \Longrightarrow\ \theta_{2,\text{blue}}\fallingdotseq34.68^\circ $$

The deviation from the incident direction is $60^\circ-34.89^\circ=25.11^\circ$ for red light and $60^\circ-34.68^\circ=25.32^\circ$ for blue light, so blue light approaches the normal more, by $0.21^\circ$ (its angle of refraction is smaller by $0.21^\circ$).

← Back to Chapter 14, “Reflection and Refraction of Light — Phase Changes, Fermat's Principle, and Total Internal Reflection”

Chapter 15 Lenses and Image Formation

Exercise 15.1: Deriving the lens equation with a third ray (the case $a\gt f$)

Solution

Place an object of height $h_A$ at a position $a\gt f$ in front of a convex lens of focal length $f$. Consider two light rays emerging from the tip of the object. The first is the ray that passes through the front focal point $F$ and enters the lens. For a thin lens, a ray passing through the front focal point $F$ travels parallel to the optical axis after refraction (this is the parallel ray that passes through the back focal point, with the path of the light traced in the reverse direction). The second is the ray that passes through the center of the lens and goes straight on (because the region near the center of a thin lens can be regarded as a thin plate whose front and back surfaces are nearly parallel, so the change of direction caused by refraction is negligible). The point where these two rays intersect is the tip of the image, and the foot of the image lies on the optical axis. The image is a real image (inverted, since $a\gt f$), and we let its height (its distance from the optical axis) be $h_B$.

First focus on the first ray. Since this ray becomes parallel to the optical axis after refraction, the height above the optical axis at which it leaves the lens equals the height $h_B$ of the tip of the image. Compare the following two right triangles.

  • The right triangle formed by three points: the foot of the object, the tip of the object, and the front focal point $F$. The right angle is at the foot of the object, and the two legs are $a-f$ (the distance from the foot to $F$) and $h_A$.
  • The right triangle formed by three points: the center of the lens, the point where this ray leaves the lens (at height $h_B$ above the optical axis), and $F$. The right angle is at the center of the lens, and the two legs are $f$ (the distance from the center of the lens to $F$) and $h_B$.

The ray forms a single straight line from the tip of the object through $F$ to the point where it leaves the lens, and $F$ lies on that line between the two; hence the angles of the two triangles at $F$ are equal as vertical angles. Since each triangle also has a right angle with respect to the optical axis, they are similar by the AA similarity criterion, and the ratios of corresponding sides are equal, so we have

$$ h_A:h_B=(a-f):f \qquad\cdots\text{①} $$

Next focus on the second ray (the ray that passes through the center and goes straight). The right triangle formed by the tip of the object, the foot of the object, and the center of the lens, and the right triangle formed by the tip of the image, the foot of the image, and the center of the lens, have equal angles at the center of the lens as vertical angles, and each has a right angle with the optical axis; hence they are similar by the AA similarity criterion, and

$$ a:b=h_A:h_B \qquad\cdots\text{②} $$

Since the ratios in ① and ② are both equal to $h_A:h_B$, we obtain

$$ a:b=(a-f):f $$

By “the product of the means equals the product of the extremes,” $a\,f=b\,(a-f)$. Dividing both sides by $abf$ (the quantities $a,b,f$ are all positive, none of them zero), we obtain

$$ \frac{af}{abf}=\frac{b(a-f)}{abf} \qquad\therefore\quad \frac{1}{b}=\frac{a-f}{af}=\frac{a}{af}-\frac{f}{af}=\frac{1}{f}-\frac{1}{a} $$

Transposing terms, we obtain

$$ \frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{b}=\frac{1}{f} $$

This is the same lens equation as the one in the main text of Section 15.2 (the derivation using a parallel ray and a ray through the center). We have confirmed that whichever two reference rays we choose, we obtain the same image position.

Exercise 15.2: Deriving the lens equation for a concave lens

Solution

Place an object of height $h_A$ at a distance $a$ in front of a concave lens whose focal length has magnitude $\abs{f}$. A ray from the tip of the object that is incident parallel to the optical axis diverges after refraction as if it had spread out from the front focal point $F$ (on the object side of the lens, at distance $\abs{f}$) (a basic property of a concave lens). Another ray, the one passing through the center of the lens, goes straight. These two rays do not intersect behind the lens, but if we extend the ray that was parallel to the axis backward, it passes through $F$ and meets the (actual path of the) ray through the center at a single point. This intersection is the tip of the virtual image, and for the time being we denote the distance from the lens to the virtual image by the positive quantity (length) $\abs{b}$.

Consider the similar triangles formed by the ray through the center (the triangle with vertices at the tip of the object, the foot of the object, and the center of the lens, and the triangle with vertices at the tip of the virtual image, the foot of the virtual image, and the center of the lens). The object and the virtual image are on the same side of the lens, so the angles at the center of the lens are equal as a common angle formed by the same straight line. Each also has a right angle with the optical axis, so they are similar by the AA similarity criterion, and

$$ a:\abs{b}=h_A:h_B $$

holds. Also, consider the right triangle formed by the point where the parallel ray passes through the lens (at height $h_A$), the center of the lens, and the front focal point $F$, and the right triangle formed by the tip of the virtual image, the foot of the virtual image, and $F$. Their angles at $F$ are equal as a common angle (formed by the same single dashed line), and each also has a right angle with the optical axis, so they satisfy the AA similarity criterion, and

$$ \abs{f}:(\abs{f}-\abs{b})=h_A:h_B $$

holds (the distance from $F$ to the virtual image is $\abs{f}-\abs{b}$ because $F$ is at distance $\abs{f}$ from the lens and the virtual image is at distance $\abs{b}$ from the lens; and since $\abs{b}\lt\abs{f}$ always holds for a concave lens, $\abs{f}-\abs{b}\gt0$. We will confirm this again at the end of the calculation). Since the right-hand sides of the two proportions are both $h_A:h_B$, we obtain

$$ a:\abs{b}=\abs{f}:(\abs{f}-\abs{b}) $$

and, by “the product of the means equals the product of the extremes,” we have

$$ \abs{b}\,\abs{f}=a\,(\abs{f}-\abs{b}) $$

Dividing both sides by $a\abs{b}\abs{f}$, we obtain

$$ \frac{1}{a}=\frac{\abs{f}-\abs{b}}{\abs{b}\abs{f}}=\frac{\abs{f}}{\abs{b}\abs{f}}-\frac{\abs{b}}{\abs{b}\abs{f}}=\frac{1}{\abs{b}}-\frac{1}{\abs{f}} $$

Subtracting $1/\abs{b}$ from both sides, we obtain

$$ \frac{1}{a}-\frac{1}{\abs{b}}=-\frac{1}{\abs{f}} $$

The right-hand side is negative, so $1/a\lt 1/\abs{b}$, that is, $a\gt\abs{b}$, and the magnitude $\abs{b}/a$ of the magnification is always less than 1.

Now, to match the sign convention of Table 15.1 of Chapter 15, we write the image distance of the virtual image as the signed quantity $b=-\abs{b}$ ($b\lt 0$) and the focal length of the concave lens as $f=-\abs{f}$ ($f\lt 0$). Since $-1/\abs{b}=1/b$ and $-1/\abs{f}=1/f$, we obtain

$$ \frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{b}=\frac{1}{f}\qquad(b\lt 0,\ f\lt 0) $$

This has exactly the same form as Formula 15.2 of Chapter 15 (the lens equation for a concave lens, the unified formula).

Finally, solving the above equation for $\abs{b}$ gives $\abs{b}=a\abs{f}/(a+\abs{f})$, and comparing this with $\abs{f}$, we obtain

$$ \abs{f}-\abs{b}=\abs{f}-\frac{a\abs{f}}{a+\abs{f}}=\frac{\abs{f}(a+\abs{f})-a\abs{f}}{a+\abs{f}}=\frac{\abs{f}^2}{a+\abs{f}}\gt0 $$

so indeed $\abs{b}\lt\abs{f}$ ($\abs{f}-\abs{b}\gt0$) holds, which confirms what we assumed along the way. Furthermore,

$$ a-\abs{b}=a-\frac{a\abs{f}}{a+\abs{f}}=\frac{a^2}{a+\abs{f}}\gt0 $$

so $a\gt\abs{b}$; that is, we can confirm that the magnitude of the magnification satisfies $\abs{b}/a\lt1$ always, regardless of the values of the object distance $a$ and the magnitude $\abs{f}$ of the focal length.

Exercise 15.3: Sequential imaging with two lenses (different numerical values)

Solution

Place a convex lens $L_1$ of focal length $f_1=10\ \mathrm{cm}$ and a convex lens $L_2$ of focal length $f_2=15\ \mathrm{cm}$ a distance $d=5\ \mathrm{cm}$ apart, and put an object at $a_1=30\ \mathrm{cm}$ in front of $L_1$. Below, all lengths are computed in $\mathrm{cm}$.

Step 1 Find the position $b_1$ of the image $P_1$ formed by $L_1$ alone. Since $a_1=30\gt f_1=10$, an inverted real image should form. By the unified formula $1/a+1/b=1/f$,

$$ \frac{1}{30}+\frac{1}{b_1}=\frac{1}{10} \qquad\therefore\quad \frac{1}{b_1}=\frac{1}{10}-\frac{1}{30}=\frac{3-1}{30}=\frac{2}{30}=\frac{1}{15} $$

we get $b_1=15\ \mathrm{cm}$. The inverted real image formed by $L_1$ lies $15\ \mathrm{cm}$ behind $L_1$.

Step 2 Find the object distance $a_2$ as seen from $L_2$. The distance between $L_1,L_2$ is $d=5\ \mathrm{cm}$, and the image formed by $L_1$ would have formed $15\ \mathrm{cm}$ behind $L_1$, that is, $15-5=10\ \mathrm{cm}$ behind $L_2$. In reality, however, the light is intercepted before that (at the position of $L_2$), so for $L_2$ the point $P_1$ is a virtual object, and

$$ a_2=-(b_1-d)=-(15-5)=-10\ \mathrm{cm} $$

This is the object distance for the second lens.

Step 3 Find the position $b_2$ of the image formed by $L_2$ (a convex lens, $f_2=15\ \mathrm{cm}$).

$$ \frac{1}{-10}+\frac{1}{b_2}=\frac{1}{15} \qquad\therefore\quad \frac{1}{b_2}=\frac{1}{15}+\frac{1}{10}=\frac{2+3}{30}=\frac{5}{30}=\frac{1}{6} $$

we get $b_2=6\ \mathrm{cm}$. Since $b_2\gt0$, the final image is a real image $6\ \mathrm{cm}$ behind $L_2$.

The overall magnification is the product of the (signed) magnifications of the individual lenses, $m_1=-b_1/a_1$ and $m_2=-b_2/a_2$:

$$ m=m_1m_2=\left(-\frac{15}{30}\right)\left(-\frac{6}{-10}\right)=\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)\times\frac{3}{5}=-\frac{3}{10}=-0.3 $$

Since $m\lt0$, the image is inverted, and its size is $\abs{m}=0.3$ times that of the object. Looking at each lens separately, the image of $L_1$ is inverted relative to the object ($m_1=-1/2\lt 0$); for $L_2$, the object ($P_1$) is a virtual object ($a_2\lt0$) and the image is a real image ($b_2\gt0$)—the signs being opposite—so the image is upright relative to $P_1$ ($m_2=+3/5\gt0$), and the final image remains inverted relative to the original object.

Ans. An inverted real image, $0.3$ times the size of the object, forms $6\ \mathrm{cm}$ behind $L_2$.

Exercise 15.4: Combined power of lenses in contact

Solution

(1) By the definition of power, $D=1/f$ ($f$ in meters), taking $f_1=25\ \mathrm{cm}=0.25\ \mathrm{m}$ for the convex lens and, noting the sign, $f_2=-50\ \mathrm{cm}=-0.50\ \mathrm{m}$ for the concave lens, we obtain

$$ D_1=\frac{1}{f_1}=\frac{1}{0.25}=4.0\ \mathrm{D}, \qquad D_2=\frac{1}{f_2}=\frac{1}{-0.50}=-2.0\ \mathrm{D} $$

These are the powers of the two lenses.

(2) With the method that uses Formula 15.3 ($1/f_{\mathrm{c}}=1/f_1+1/f_2$), keeping the lengths in $\mathrm{cm}$, we have

$$ \frac{1}{f_{\mathrm{c}}}=\frac{1}{25}+\frac{1}{-50}=\frac{2}{50}-\frac{1}{50}=\frac{1}{50} \qquad\therefore\quad f_{\mathrm{c}}=50\ \mathrm{cm}=0.50\ \mathrm{m} $$

On the other hand, with the method that finds the result from the sum of the powers, we have

$$ D=D_1+D_2=4.0+(-2.0)=2.0\ \mathrm{D} \qquad\therefore\quad f_{\mathrm{c}}=\frac{1}{D}=\frac{1}{2.0}=0.50\ \mathrm{m}=50\ \mathrm{cm} $$

Thus the two methods indeed agree: both give $f_{\mathrm{c}}=50\ \mathrm{cm}$ (reading both sides of Formula 15.3 in terms of power gives $D=D_1+D_2$ itself, so this agreement is only natural). Since $f_{\mathrm{c}}\gt0$, the combined lens acts as a convex lens.

Exercise 15.5: The difference between real and virtual images

Solution

As we confirmed in Definition 15.3 of Chapter 15, a real image is “an image formed when the light emerging from the object actually converges to a single point after passing through the lens.” For example, in Figure 15.3 (a convex lens with $a\gt f$), the rays emerging from the tip of the object (the green and blue solid lines) really do meet, after passing through the lens, at a single point behind the lens (the tip of the image). If a screen or a sheet of paper is placed at this point, light energy actually gathers there, so a sharp image appears. Countless rays pass through the lens, but all the light emerging from one point of the object (for a thin lens, under the paraxial approximation) converges to this same single point, so if the screen is placed at that position, every ray arrives at the same point and a bright image forms.

In contrast, as we confirmed in Figure 15.4 (a convex lens with $0\lt a\lt f$) and Figure 15.6 (a concave lens), in the case of a virtual image the rays after passing through the lens (solid lines) only spread farther apart from each other behind the lens, and do not actually converge to any single point. The position of the virtual image is found as the point where the extensions of these rays meet when they are extended backward with dashed lines, in a direction that the light does not actually travel (behind the lens, toward the object side). However, these extended parts are not paths along which light actually travels—they are merely hypothetical lines used in the construction, meaning “if the light extended this far, it would look as though it had started from this point.”

Therefore, even if a screen is placed at the position of the virtual image, no actual light converges to a single point there (the light merely passes by, diverging behind the lens), so nothing appears. Wherever the screen is moved behind the lens, no image appears, for the same reason. When the eye looks at this diverging light, the human visual system, through the brain's mechanism of interpreting light as having traveled in a straight line, perceives the object as if it were at the position of the virtual image (an apparent image seen only when looking into the lens), but this is a phenomenon that occurs only inside the eye, and it has no effect on a physical detector such as a screen. This is the reason why “a real image appears on a screen, but a virtual image does not.”

Exercise 15.6: Total magnification of a microscope

Solution

For the objective lens, with focal length $f_{\text{obj}}=1.0\ \mathrm{cm}$ and object distance $a_1=1.2\ \mathrm{cm}$ (since $a_1\gt f_{\text{obj}}$, an inverted real image forms), using Formula 15.1 of Chapter 15 (the unified formula) with lengths in $\mathrm{cm}$, we have

$$ \frac{1}{1.2}+\frac{1}{b_1}=\frac{1}{1.0} \qquad\therefore\quad \frac{1}{b_1}=1-\frac{1}{1.2}=1-\frac56=\frac16 $$

so $b_1=6.0\ \mathrm{cm}$. The magnitude of the magnification of the objective lens is

$$ \abs{m_{\text{obj}}}=\left|\frac{b_1}{a_1}\right|=\frac{6.0}{1.2}=5.0 $$

This is the magnitude of the magnification of the objective lens.

The eyepiece observes this intermediate image $P_1$ as a magnifying glass. When we observe so that the virtual image forms at the distance of distinct vision $D_0=25\ \mathrm{cm}$, the angular magnification is, by Formula 15.4 of Chapter 15,

$$ M_{\text{eye}}=1+\frac{D_0}{f_{\text{eye}}}=1+\frac{25}{5.0}=1+5.0=6.0 $$

This is the angular magnification of the eyepiece. The total magnification of the whole microscope is, by Eq. (15.5) of Chapter 15 ($M_{\text{total}}=\abs{m_{\text{obj}}}\times M_{\text{eye}}$),

$$ M_{\text{total}}=5.0\times6.0=30 $$

That is, this microscope magnifies the object under observation by a factor of 30.

Exercise 15.7: The combined focal length of lenses in contact, and the combined focal length when the lenses are separated

Solution

(1) Let the object distance be $a_1$. For $L_1$ (focal length $f_1$), the unified formula gives

$$ \frac{1}{a_1}+\frac{1}{b_1}=\frac{1}{f_1}\qquad\cdots\text{①} $$

The gap between the lenses is $d=0$, so the object distance of the object $P_1$ for $L_2$ is $a_2=-(b_1-d)=-b_1$ ($P_1$ would have formed at a position $b_1$ behind $L_2$, so it is a virtual object). Writing the unified formula for $L_2$ (focal length $f_2$), we get

$$ \frac{1}{-b_1}+\frac{1}{b_2}=\frac{1}{f_2}\qquad\cdots\text{②} $$

Adding ① and ② side by side, $1/b_1$ and $-1/b_1$ cancel, and we obtain

$$ \frac{1}{a_1}+\frac{1}{b_2}=\frac{1}{f_1}+\frac{1}{f_2} $$

The left-hand side is exactly $1/(\text{object distance})+1/(\text{image distance})$ when the whole system is regarded as a single lens of combined focal length $f_{\mathrm{c}}$, so comparing with $1/a_1+1/b_2=1/f_{\mathrm{c}}$, we obtain

$$ \frac{1}{f_{\mathrm{c}}}=\frac{1}{f_1}+\frac{1}{f_2} $$

This is the desired result.

(2) As we saw in the box “Why do parallel rays converge to a single point?” in Section 15.1, a thin lens of focal length $f$ bends a ray passing at height $h$ above the optical axis toward the axis by the angle $h/f$ (paraxial approximation). Follow the ray that enters $L_1$ parallel to the optical axis at height $h$. We measure the slope in the direction toward the optical axis (if the slope is $s$, then after advancing a distance $x$ the height gets closer to the optical axis by $sx$).

  • Slope just after leaving $L_1$: $s_1=0+\dfrac{h}{f_1}=\dfrac{h}{f_1}$.
  • Height at which the ray enters $L_2$: while the ray travels the distance $d$ from $L_1$, its height gets closer to the optical axis by $s_1d$, so $h_2=h-s_1d=h\left(1-\dfrac{d}{f_1}\right)$.
  • Slope just after leaving $L_2$: $s_2=s_1+\dfrac{h_2}{f_2}=\dfrac{h}{f_1}+\dfrac{h}{f_2}\left(1-\dfrac{d}{f_1}\right)$.

If the whole lens system is regarded as a single lens of focal length $f_{\mathrm{c}}$, a parallel ray at height $h$ should be bent with the slope $s_2=h/f_{\mathrm{c}}$, so

$$ \frac{1}{f_{\mathrm{c}}}=\frac{s_2}{h}=\frac{1}{f_1}+\frac{1}{f_2}-\frac{d}{f_1f_2} $$

has been shown. For $d=0$ it agrees with the result of (1). When $f_1=15\ \mathrm{cm}$, $f_2=10\ \mathrm{cm}$, and $d=5\ \mathrm{cm}$,

$$ \frac{1}{f_{\mathrm{c}}}=\frac{1}{15}+\frac{1}{10}-\frac{5}{15\times10}=\frac{10+15-5}{150}=\frac{20}{150}=\frac{2}{15} \qquad\therefore\quad f_{\mathrm{c}}=\frac{15}{2}=7.5\ \mathrm{cm} $$

(Verification: by the sequential-imaging method, parallel rays coming from infinity try to converge at $b_1=15\ \mathrm{cm}$ behind $L_1$, and $a_2=d-b_1=-10\ \mathrm{cm}$ and $1/b_2=1/f_2-1/a_2=1/10-1/(-10)=1/5$ give $b_2=5\ \mathrm{cm}$. This $5\ \mathrm{cm}$ is the distance to the back focal point measured from $L_2$, and it agrees with $f_{\mathrm{c}}(1-d/f_1)=7.5\times(1-1/3)=5\ \mathrm{cm}$.)

Exercise 15.8: What does it mean that the image “disappears” at $a=f$?

Solution

(1) Draw the two reference rays emerging from the tip of the object. Let the height of the object be $h_A$ and let $a=f$.

  • The ray parallel to the optical axis: it goes from the point at height $h_A$ on the lens surface toward the back focal point $F'$ (on the optical axis, at distance $f$ from the lens), so its slope after leaving the lens (in the direction approaching the optical axis) is $h_A/f$.
  • The ray through the center: it goes straight from the tip of the object (at distance $a=f$ in front of the lens, at height $h_A$) to the center of the lens, so its slope (in the direction approaching the optical axis) is $h_A/a=h_A/f$.

Since the slopes of the two rays are equal ($h_A/f$), the two rays after leaving the lens are exactly parallel and never intersect, however far they go. That is, the light emerging from the tip of the object does not converge to a single point after passing through the lens but proceeds as parallel rays. For the image to “disappear” means that the position $b$ where the image would form jumps to infinity ($b=\infty$ from $1/b=1/f-1/a=0$), so that no image is formed wherever a screen is placed.

(2) We compute lengths in $\mathrm{cm}$. When $f=10$ and $a=10.5$,

$$ \frac{1}{b}=\frac{1}{10}-\frac{1}{10.5}=\frac{10.5-10}{105}=\frac{0.5}{105}=\frac{1}{210} \qquad\therefore\quad b=210\ \mathrm{cm},\quad m=-\frac{b}{a}=-\frac{210}{10.5}=-20 $$

and an inverted real image magnified 20 times forms $210\ \mathrm{cm}$ behind the lens. When $a=9.5$,

$$ \frac{1}{b}=\frac{1}{10}-\frac{1}{9.5}=\frac{9.5-10}{95}=-\frac{0.5}{95}=-\frac{1}{190} \qquad\therefore\quad b=-190\ \mathrm{cm},\quad m=-\frac{b}{a}=+\frac{190}{9.5}=+20 $$

and an upright virtual image magnified 20 times forms $190\ \mathrm{cm}$ in front of the lens. Therefore, when the object is brought closer from outside the focal point, the real image rapidly moves away to the positive side ($b\to+\infty$); at $a=f$ the light becomes parallel rays and the image vanishes; and once the object enters the inside of the focal point, the virtual image comes back from the side near negative infinity. That the curve for the convex lens in Figure 15.7 has an asymptote at $a=f$, and has $b\lt0$ for $a\lt f$ and $b\gt0$ for $a\gt f$, expresses this switch.

← Back to Chapter 15, “Lenses and Image Formation”

Chapter 16 Interference of Light and the Diffraction Grating — Young's Experiment, Thin Films, and Newton's Rings

Exercise 16.1: Deriving the path-difference formula for Young's double-slit experiment

Solution

Let $\ell_1=\mathrm{S}_1\mathrm{P},\ \ell_2=\mathrm{S}_2\mathrm{P}$ be the distances from the slits $\mathrm{S}_1,\mathrm{S}_2$ (separation $d$) to a point $\mathrm{P}$ on the screen, respectively. Let $\ell$ be the distance from the slit plate to the screen, let $\mathrm{O}$ be the center of the screen (directly in front of the midpoint $\mathrm{S}$ of $\mathrm{S}_1,\mathrm{S}_2$), let $x$ be the distance from $\mathrm{O}$ to $\mathrm{P}$, and let $\theta$ be the angle between $\mathrm{SO}$ and $\mathrm{SP}$.

Under the condition $d\ll\ell$ (the slit separation is much smaller than the slit-to-screen distance), the three lines $\mathrm{S}_1\mathrm{P},\mathrm{S}_2\mathrm{P},\mathrm{SP}$ can be regarded as almost parallel. Drop a perpendicular from $\mathrm{S}_1$ to the line $\mathrm{S}_2\mathrm{P}$ and let $\mathrm{H}$ be its foot. Since $\mathrm{P}$ is far away, $\mathrm{H}$ and $\mathrm{S}_1$ are at almost the same distance from $\mathrm{P}$, so the extra distance traveled by the light from $\mathrm{S}_2$ is $\mathrm{S}_2\mathrm{H}$: $|\ell_2-\ell_1|\fallingdotseq\mathrm{S}_2\mathrm{H}$.

The right triangle $\mathrm{S}_1\mathrm{S}_2\mathrm{H}$ has hypotenuse $\mathrm{S}_1\mathrm{S}_2=d$, and its angle at $\mathrm{S}_1$ is $\theta$ (since $\mathrm{S}_1\mathrm{S}_2\perp\mathrm{SO}$ and $\mathrm{S}_1\mathrm{H}\perp\mathrm{SP}$, each pair of perpendicular lines is obtained from the other by rotating through the same angle, so the angle between $\mathrm{S}_1\mathrm{S}_2$ and $\mathrm{S}_1\mathrm{H}$ also equals the angle $\theta$ between $\mathrm{SO}$ and $\mathrm{SP}$). In a right triangle, “opposite side $=$ hypotenuse $\times\sin(\text{opposite angle})$,” so since the hypotenuse is $d$ and the opposite angle is $\theta$, we obtain

$$ |\ell_2-\ell_1|\fallingdotseq d\sin\theta $$

Next, from the condition $x\ll\ell$ (the range viewed on the screen is much smaller than the slit-to-screen distance), the angle $\theta$ is small. When angles are measured in radians, the three values $\sin\theta,\tan\theta,\theta$ approach one another as the angle approaches 0 (a basic approximation from radian measure in high school mathematics), so we may make the replacement

$$ \sin\theta\fallingdotseq\tan\theta $$

On the other hand, considering the right triangle $\mathrm{SOP}$ ($\mathrm{SO}=\ell$, $\mathrm{OP}=x$), we have $\tan\theta=(\text{opposite})/(\text{adjacent})=x/\ell$. Therefore,

$$ \sin\theta\fallingdotseq\tan\theta=\frac{x}{\ell} $$

Substituting this into the previous equation gives

$$ |\ell_2-\ell_1|\fallingdotseq d\sin\theta\fallingdotseq d\cdot\frac{x}{\ell}=\frac{dx}{\ell} $$

and Eq. (16.1) of Chapter 16 (Section 16.2) has been derived. We used two approximations: (1) “$\mathrm{S}_1\mathrm{P},\mathrm{S}_2\mathrm{P},\mathrm{SP}$ are almost parallel,” which follows from $d\ll\ell$, and (2) “$\sin\theta\fallingdotseq\tan\theta$,” which follows from $x\ll\ell$ (and hence $\theta$ is small).

Exercise 16.2: Deriving the bright and dark conditions for thin-film interference

Solution

Consider monochromatic light (wavelength $\lambda$ in vacuum) incident almost perpendicularly (angle of incidence $\theta_{\mathrm{i}}\fallingdotseq0$, and hence angle of refraction inside the film $\theta_{\mathrm{t}}\fallingdotseq0$) on a thin film of refractive index $n$ and thickness $d$ (surrounded by air). We compare two rays: the light reflected at the top surface of the film, and the light that enters the film, is reflected at the bottom surface, and then returns to the top surface. The latter travels an extra optical path length of $n\times2d$ inside the film (we used the fact that inside a medium of refractive index $n$ light travels an optical path length $n$ times that in vacuum, and that at normal incidence the round-trip distance is simply $2d$), so the optical path difference between the two rays is

$$ 2nd $$

(For a general angle of incidence this becomes $2nd\cos\theta_{\mathrm{t}}$, but here we assumed almost normal incidence and set $\cos\theta_{\mathrm{t}}\fallingdotseq1$.)

Next, we check the phase changes on reflection. The reflection at the top surface of the film occurs at the boundary going from air (small refractive index) to the film (large refractive index; assume $n\gt1$), so it is a reflection at a fixed end, and the phase changes by $\pi$ (half a wavelength). The reflection at the bottom surface of the film occurs at the boundary going from the film (large refractive index) to the air beneath it (small refractive index), so it is a reflection at a free end, and the phase does not change.

Therefore only the light reflected at the top surface carries an extra phase $\pi$, which corresponds to a phase shift equivalent to a path difference of half a wavelength, $\lambda/2$. For the two reflected rays actually to reinforce each other (crests overlap crests, troughs overlap troughs), the “effective path difference,” obtained by combining the optical path difference $2nd$ with this extra half wavelength $\lambda/2$, must be an integer multiple of the wavelength:

$$ 2nd+\frac{\lambda}{2}=m\lambda\qquad(m=1,2,3,\ldots) $$

(Taking $m=0$ would give $d\lt0$, which is not admissible, so $m$ starts from 1.) Subtracting $\lambda/2$ from both sides and rearranging, we get

$$ 2nd=m\lambda-\frac{\lambda}{2}=\left(m-\frac12\right)\lambda $$

Now let us redefine $m'=m-1$. Then $m-\tfrac12=m'+\tfrac12$, and $m'=0,1,2,\ldots$. Therefore the bright-fringe condition is

$$ 2nd=\left(m'+\frac12\right)\lambda\qquad(m'=0,1,2,\ldots) $$

(Below, we write $m'$ as $m$ again.)

Conversely, the condition for the rays to cancel each other (a crest overlaps a trough) is that the effective path difference $2nd+\lambda/2$ be a half-integer multiple of the wavelength:

$$ 2nd+\frac{\lambda}{2}=\left(m+\frac12\right)\lambda\ \Rightarrow\ 2nd=m\lambda\qquad(m=0,1,2,\ldots) $$

This agrees with Law 16.2 of the main text (for a general angle of incidence, the optical path difference is replaced by $2nd\cos\theta_{\mathrm{t}}$). In ordinary interference without phase changes, the conditions for bright and dark fringes are the reverse of these (a path difference of $m\lambda$ gives a bright fringe, and $(m+\frac12)\lambda$ gives a dark fringe). Comparing with this, we see that because “only one of the two reflections is at a fixed end and shifts the phase by $\pi$,” the conditions are exactly interchanged.

Exercise 16.3: Measuring the thickness of a thin foil with an air wedge

Solution

Let $d$ be the thickness of the air layer at horizontal distance $x$ from the apex of the wedge. The dark-fringe condition (Law 16.3) can then be written $2d=m\lambda$ ($m=0,1,2,\ldots$). The apex ($d=0$) is the $m=0$ dark fringe, which, as stated in the problem, is not counted, so the 32nd dark fringe counted from the apex corresponds to $m=32$. The end where the foil is inserted (where the air layer has thickness $d_{\mathrm{f}}$) is exactly this 32nd fringe, so

$$ 2d_{\mathrm{f}}=32\lambda $$

Substituting $\lambda=500\ \mathrm{nm}=500\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm{m}$, we obtain

$$ d_{\mathrm{f}}=\frac{32\lambda}{2}=\frac{32\times(500\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm{m})}{2}=8.00\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{m}=8.00\ \mathrm{\mu m} $$

The thickness of the metal foil is thus found to be about 8 micrometers.

Since the wedge angle is $\theta\fallingdotseq d_{\mathrm{f}}/L=(8.00\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{m})/(0.0800\ \mathrm{m})=1.00\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{rad}$, Eq. (16.11) of the main text gives the spacing of the dark fringes as

$$ \Delta x=\frac{\lambda}{2\theta}=\frac{500\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm{m}}{2\times1.00\times10^{-4}}=2.5\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{m}=2.5\ \mathrm{mm} $$

This agrees with $L/32=80.0\ \mathrm{mm}/32=2.5\ \mathrm{mm}$, and is consistent with the situation in which 32 dark fringes line up over the length $L$.

Exercise 16.4: Geometric derivation of the formula for the optical path difference in Newton's rings

Solution

We regard the spherical surface of the plano-convex lens as part of a sphere with center $\mathrm{O}$ and radius $R$. Let $\mathrm{A}$ be the topmost point of the spherical surface and $\mathrm{B}$ the point of contact between the lens and the flat glass plate ($\mathrm{A},\mathrm{O},\mathrm{B}$ lie on the same vertical line, and $\mathrm{AB}=2R$ is a diameter of the sphere). On this axis $\mathrm{AB}$, let $\mathrm{D}$ be the point at height $d$ (the thickness of the air layer) above $\mathrm{B}$ ($\mathrm{DB}=d$, $\mathrm{AD}=2R-d$), and let $\mathrm{C}$ be the point where the horizontal line through $\mathrm{D}$ meets the spherical surface. Since the thickness of the air layer at horizontal distance $r$ from the axis is $d$, we have $\mathrm{DC}=r$.

(1) Since $\mathrm{AB}$ is a diameter of the circle, the inscribed angle subtending a diameter is a right angle (high school mathematics), so $\angle\mathrm{ACB}=90^{\circ}$.

(2) Since $\mathrm{CD}$ is perpendicular to the axis $\mathrm{AB}$, the angle at $\mathrm{D}$ is a right angle in both $\triangle\mathrm{ACD}$ and $\triangle\mathrm{CBD}$.

(3) Since the acute angles of the right triangle $\mathrm{ACB}$ sum to $90^{\circ}$, $\angle\mathrm{CAD}+\angle\mathrm{CBD}=90^{\circ}$. In the right triangle $\mathrm{CBD}$ as well, $\angle\mathrm{DCB}+\angle\mathrm{CBD}=90^{\circ}$. Therefore $\angle\mathrm{CAD}=\angle\mathrm{DCB}$.

(4) Hence the two right triangles have two equal angles, so $\triangle\mathrm{ACD}\sim\triangle\mathrm{CBD}$ ($\mathrm{A}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{C}$, $\mathrm{C}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{B}$, $\mathrm{D}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{D}$). Taking the ratio of corresponding sides gives

$$ \mathrm{AD}:\mathrm{DC}=\mathrm{CD}:\mathrm{DB}\ \Rightarrow\ (2R-d):r=r:d $$

Using the property of proportions (the product of the means $=$ the product of the extremes), we get

$$ r\times r=(2R-d)\times d $$ $$ \therefore\ r^2=2dR-d^2 $$

Since $d$ is far smaller than the radius of curvature $R$ of the lens, which is on the order of magnitude of a few meters ($d\sim10^{-6}\ \mathrm{m}$ against $R\sim1\ \mathrm{m}$ or so), the $d^2$ term can be neglected compared with the $2dR$ term:

$$ r^2\fallingdotseq2dR $$

This is the desired equation. Therefore the optical path difference (the round-trip distance in the air layer) is $2d\fallingdotseq r^2/R$.

(Alternative solution 1: the intersecting chords theorem. The chord $\mathrm{CC'}$ ($\mathrm{C'}$ is the point on the spherical surface on the opposite side of the axis from $\mathrm{C}$) and the diameter $\mathrm{AB}$ meet at $\mathrm{D}$, so $\mathrm{DC}\cdot\mathrm{DC'}=\mathrm{DA}\cdot\mathrm{DB}$. Since $\mathrm{DC}=\mathrm{DC'}=r$, we get $r^2=(2R-d)\,d$.)

(Alternative solution 2: a method using coordinates. Take the contact point $\mathrm{B}$ as the origin, the $y$ axis vertically upward, and the $x$ axis horizontal. The center of the sphere is $(0,R)$, so the spherical surface satisfies $x^2+(y-R)^2=R^2$. The position where the thickness of the air layer is $d$ is $y=d$, and the horizontal distance $x=r$ from the axis there satisfies $r^2+(d-R)^2=R^2$, so

$$ r^2=R^2-(d-R)^2=2Rd-d^2 $$

and we obtain the same result.)

Exercise 16.5: The difference in phase change between reflected and transmitted Newton's rings

Solution

The case of reflected Newton's rings: The two reflected rays that reach the observer are (a) the ray reflected at the lower spherical surface of the lens (the glass → air boundary) and (b) the ray reflected at the upper surface of the flat glass plate (the air → glass boundary). Since (a) is a reflection going from a medium of large refractive index to one of small refractive index, it is a reflection at a free end (no phase change), and since (b) is a reflection going from a medium of small refractive index to one of large refractive index, it is a reflection at a fixed end (the phase changes by $\pi$). Because only one of the two rays acquires the phase shift $\pi$, where the path difference is 0 (the center, $r=0$) the two rays meet in antiphase (crest and trough) and cancel each other—the center is a dark fringe.

The case of transmitted Newton's rings: The two transmitted rays that reach the observer are (c) the ray that passes straight through the lens, the air layer, and the glass plate, and (d) the ray that is first reflected at the upper surface of the flat glass plate (a fixed-end reflection at the air → glass boundary, with a phase change of $\pi$), is then reflected again at the lower spherical surface of the lens (this too is a reflection at the air → glass boundary, hence a fixed-end reflection, with a further phase change of $\pi$), and is then transmitted. The ray (d) undergoes two fixed-end reflections along its path, so the total phase change is $\pi+\pi=2\pi$. A phase change of $2\pi$ (one full period) is completely indistinguishable, as far as the shape of the wave is concerned, from no change at all ($\cos(\phi+2\pi)=\cos\phi$). Therefore the two transmitted rays (c) and (d) effectively meet in phase, and where the path difference is 0 (the center, $r=0$) they reinforce each other—the center is a bright fringe.

From the viewpoint of energy conservation: The energy of the light incident at the center should be transmitted if it is not reflected. Indeed, in reflection the center is a dark fringe (cancellation = little reflected light), whereas in transmission the center is a bright fringe (reinforcement = much transmitted light), and the relation that transmission is strengthened by exactly as much as reflection is weakened is consistent with the law of conservation of energy. Rather, the fact that the conditions for reflection and transmission are interchanged is a result that the law of conservation of energy naturally demands.

Exercise 16.6: Minimum thickness of an antireflection coating

Solution

The reflection at the top surface of the film (air $n=1.0$ → film $n_{\mathrm{f}}=1.45$) is a reflection at a fixed end (phase change $\pi$), and the reflection at the bottom surface of the film (film $n_{\mathrm{f}}=1.45$ → glass $n_{\mathrm{glass}}=1.52$) is also a reflection at a fixed end (phase change $\pi$) because $n_{\mathrm{f}}\lt n_{\mathrm{glass}}$. Since both are fixed-end reflections and the phase shifts by the same amount, the phase difference between the two reflected rays is determined by the optical path difference ($2n_{\mathrm{f}}d$) alone, and there is no extra phase change. Therefore the condition under which the reflected light cancels most completely (favorable for antireflection) is

$$ 2n_{\mathrm{f}}d=\left(m+\frac12\right)\lambda\qquad(m=0,1,2,\ldots) $$

For the thinnest film ($m=0$), $2n_{\mathrm{f}}d=\lambda/2$, so

$$ d=\frac{\lambda}{4n_{\mathrm{f}}} $$

Substituting $\lambda=520\ \mathrm{nm}$ and $n_{\mathrm{f}}=1.45$, we obtain

$$ d=\frac{520\ \mathrm{nm}}{4\times1.45}=\frac{520}{5.80}\ \mathrm{nm}\approx89.7\ \mathrm{nm} $$

Thus a film thickness of about 90 nanometers, much thinner than the wavelength of visible light, is required.

Exercise 16.7: Width of the principal maximum of a diffraction grating

Solution

The interference intensity for $N$ slits is, by Eq. (16.6) of Chapter 16,

$$ I(\delta)=I_0\left(\frac{\sin(N\delta/2)}{N\sin(\delta/2)}\right)^2 $$

The principal maximum is at $\delta=0$ (or at its integer multiples $\delta=2\pi m$), where $I(0)=I_0$. We seek the point $\delta_1$ (the smallest of those with $\delta_1>0$) at which the intensity first becomes 0 slightly away from the principal maximum. $I(\delta)=0$ occurs when the numerator satisfies $\sin(N\delta/2)=0$, and at the same time the denominator must satisfy $\sin(\delta/2)\neq0$ (otherwise we get the indeterminate form $0/0$, which in fact turns out to be the principal maximum itself).

Among the $\delta>0$ satisfying $\sin(N\delta/2)=0$, the smallest is obtained by solving $N\delta/2=\pi$ (the smallest positive case in which $N\delta/2$ is an integer multiple of $\pi$):

$$ \delta_1=\frac{2\pi}{N} $$

At this point $\delta_1/2=\pi/N$, and as long as $N\ge2$ we have $0\lt\pi/N\lt\pi$, so we can also confirm that $\sin(\delta_1/2)\neq0$ (the denominator does not vanish). Therefore the “one-sided width” from the principal maximum ($\delta=0$) to the first point where the intensity is 0 is $2\pi/N$, and the principal maximum lies within the range $-2\pi/N\lt\delta\lt2\pi/N$—that is, the full width of the principal maximum (the width between the first zeros on both sides) is

$$ \Delta\delta=\frac{4\pi}{N} $$

From this equation it follows directly that the width $\Delta\delta$ of the principal maximum is inversely proportional to the number of slits $N$ ($\Delta\delta\propto1/N$). The larger $N$ is (the more grooves there are, as in a diffraction grating, where the number of grooves is very large), the narrower and sharper the bright line of the principal maximum becomes—this is the mathematical confirmation of how the bright fringes grow sharper as the number of slits is increased through $N=2,5,20$ in Figure 16.7 of Chapter 16. (We also confirmed numerically, for $N=2,5,20,100$, that the intensity vanishes at $\delta_1=2\pi/N$.)

Exercise 16.8: Computing the interplanar spacing of lattice planes from the Bragg condition

Solution

Solving the Bragg condition $2d\sin\theta=n\lambda$ for $d$, we get

$$ d=\frac{n\lambda}{2\sin\theta} $$

Substituting $n=1$ (first order), $\lambda=0.15406\ \mathrm{nm}$, and $\theta=14.22^{\circ}$, we obtain

$$ d=\frac{1\times0.15406\ \mathrm{nm}}{2\sin14.22^{\circ}}=\frac{0.15406\ \mathrm{nm}}{2\times0.24567}\approx0.3136\ \mathrm{nm} $$

This value agrees very well with the interplanar spacing of the (111) lattice planes of a real silicon crystal (from the crystal structure with lattice constant $a=0.5431\ \mathrm{nm}$, $d_{111}=a/\sqrt3\approx0.3136\ \mathrm{nm}$), and it is the Bragg angle of the (111) reflection, which appears most strongly in the powder X-ray diffraction pattern of silicon. In this way, from measurements of the X-ray wavelength and the Bragg angle alone, the spacing between atomic planes in a crystal can be determined with a precision finer than a nanometer.

Exercise 16.9: Layer thicknesses for a multilayer interference filter

Solution

The optical path length is the product $nt$ of the refractive index $n$ and the physical thickness $t$ (Definition 16.1). Since the optical path length of each layer is to be $\lambda_0/4$, we have $nt=\lambda_0/4$, that is, $t=\lambda_0/(4n)$. With $\lambda_0=550\ \mathrm{nm}$,

$$ t_{\mathrm{TiO_2}}=\frac{550\ \mathrm{nm}}{4\times2.4}=\frac{550}{9.6}\ \mathrm{nm}\approx57.3\ \mathrm{nm} $$ $$ t_{\mathrm{SiO_2}}=\frac{550\ \mathrm{nm}}{4\times1.46}=\frac{550}{5.84}\ \mathrm{nm}\approx94.2\ \mathrm{nm} $$

A ray making one round trip through each layer travels a physical distance of $2t$, so its optical path length is $n\times2t=2\times\lambda_0/4=\lambda_0/2$. In terms of phase, since the phase advances by $2\pi$ over one wavelength $\lambda_0$, an optical path length of $\lambda_0/2$ corresponds to a phase of $2\pi\times(\lambda_0/2)/\lambda_0=\pi$. To this is added the difference $\pi$ between the phase changes on reflection at adjacent boundaries (a fixed-end reflection from low to high refractive index gives $\pi$; a free-end reflection from high to low gives 0), giving a total of $2\pi$, so the rays reflected from all the boundaries reinforce each other in phase.

Chapter 17 Superposition of Waves and Fourier Analysis — Wave Packets and Group Velocity

Exercise 17.1: A string with an initial velocity (struck by a hammer)

Solution

Since the initial displacement is $u_0\equiv0$, only the second term of d'Alembert's formula remains, giving

$$ y(x,t)=\frac{1}{2v}\int_{x-vt}^{x+vt}w_0(s)\,\dd s $$

Since $w_0(s)=w_0=2.0\ \mathrm{m/s}$ ($-a\le s\le a$, $a=0.020\ \mathrm{m}$) and it is $0$ elsewhere, the integral can be written as $y=\dfrac{w_0\,\ell}{2v}$ in terms of “the length $\ell$ of the part of the interval $[x-vt,\ x+vt]$ that overlaps $[-a,a]$.”

(1) At $t=1.0\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{s}$, $vt=100\times1.0\times10^{-4}=0.010\ \mathrm{m}$. For $x=0$ the interval is $[-0.010,\,0.010]$, which lies entirely within $[-0.020,\,0.020]$, so the overlap length is $\ell=0.020\ \mathrm{m}$. Therefore

$$ y=\frac{2.0\times0.020}{2\times100}=2.0\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{m}=0.20\ \mathrm{mm} $$

(2) At $t=5.0\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{s}$, $vt=0.050\ \mathrm{m}$. For $x=0.050\ \mathrm{m}$ the interval is $[0,\ 0.100]$, and its overlap with $[-0.020,\,0.020]$ is $[0,\,0.020]$, of length $\ell=0.020\ \mathrm{m}$. Therefore $y=\dfrac{2.0\times0.020}{2\times100}=2.0\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{m}=0.20\ \mathrm{mm}$.

(3) After a sufficiently long time, $vt\gg a$, and the interval $[-vt,\,vt]$ for $x=0$ completely contains $[-a,a]$. The overlap length is $\ell=2a=0.040\ \mathrm{m}$, and so

$$ y=\frac{2.0\times0.040}{2\times100}=4.0\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{m}=0.40\ \mathrm{mm} $$

We see that, even though the initial displacement is zero, a flat plateau of height $w_0a/v=0.40\ \mathrm{mm}$ forms between the waves spreading to the left and right from the struck portion (this is for an infinitely long string; for a real, finite string it holds only until the waves reflected at the ends return).

Exercise 17.2: Fourier series of a triangle wave

Solution

Since $y(-t)=y(t)$ (an even function), $b_n=0$. The mean value $a_0/2$ is $0$, since the area over one period is $2\int_0^{T/2}A(1-4t/T)\,\dd t=2A\bigl(T/2-T/2\bigr)=0$. With $\omega_1=2\pi/T$, the cosine coefficients are

$$ a_n=\frac4T\int_0^{T/2}A\left(1-\frac{4t}{T}\right)\cos n\omega_1t\,\dd t =\frac{4A}{T}\left[\int_0^{T/2}\cos n\omega_1t\,\dd t-\frac4T\int_0^{T/2}t\cos n\omega_1t\,\dd t\right] $$

The first integral is $\dfrac{\sin(n\omega_1T/2)}{n\omega_1}=\dfrac{\sin n\pi}{n\omega_1}=0$. For the second integral, integration by parts gives

$$ \int_0^{T/2}t\cos n\omega_1t\,\dd t=\left[\frac{t\sin n\omega_1t}{n\omega_1}\right]_0^{T/2}-\int_0^{T/2}\frac{\sin n\omega_1t}{n\omega_1}\,\dd t=0+\left[\frac{\cos n\omega_1t}{(n\omega_1)^2}\right]_0^{T/2}=\frac{(-1)^n-1}{(n\omega_1)^2} $$

Since $\omega_1=2\pi/T$, we have $(n\omega_1)^2=4\pi^2n^2/T^2$, and therefore

$$ a_n=\frac{4A}{T}\cdot\left(-\frac4T\right)\cdot\frac{(-1)^n-1}{(n\omega_1)^2}=\frac{16A}{T^2}\cdot\frac{1-(-1)^n}{4\pi^2n^2/T^2}=\frac{4A\,[1-(-1)^n]}{\pi^2n^2} $$

That is, $a_n=\dfrac{8A}{\pi^2n^2}$ ($n$ odd) and $a_n=0$ ($n$ even), and $y(t)=\dfrac{8A}{\pi^2}\displaystyle\sum_{n\,\text{odd}}\dfrac{\cos n\omega_1t}{n^2}$.

(1) Since the function is even, only cosines appear and no sines. The only harmonics that appear are those of odd order. This is because the triangle wave has half-wave symmetry $y(t+T/2)=-y(t)$ (for example, $y(0)=A$ and $y(T/2)=A(1-2)=-A$). Since $\cos\bigl(n\omega_1(t+T/2)\bigr)=(-1)^n\cos n\omega_1t$, an even-order component does not change sign under $t\to t+T/2$, and so it cannot be compatible with $y$, which changes sign. This is the same reason that a square wave contains only odd-order harmonics.

(2) With $A=3.0\ \mathrm{V}$, $a_1=\dfrac{8\times3.0}{\pi^2}\approx2.43\ \mathrm{V}$, $a_3=\dfrac{a_1}{9}\approx0.270\ \mathrm{V}$, and $a_5=\dfrac{a_1}{25}\approx0.0973\ \mathrm{V}$.

(3) The amplitudes of the harmonics of a square wave decrease as $1/n$ ($b_3/b_1=1/3$, $b_5/b_1=1/5$), and those of a triangle wave as $1/n^2$ ($a_3/a_1=1/9$, $a_5/a_1=1/25$). The triangle wave is continuous and has only corners, so its harmonics decay faster, it approaches the original waveform with few terms, and there is no Gibbs phenomenon either. A square wave has discontinuities, so its harmonics decay only slowly.

Exercise 17.3: Plucking the point at $L/4$ — harmonics and timbre

Solution

(1) Substituting $x_0=L/4$ gives $x_0(L-x_0)=\dfrac L4\cdot\dfrac{3L}{4}=\dfrac{3L^2}{16}$, so

$$ C_n=\frac{2hL^2}{n^2\pi^2}\cdot\frac{16}{3L^2}\sin\frac{n\pi}{4}=\frac{32h}{3n^2\pi^2}\sin\frac{n\pi}{4} $$

For $h=2.0\ \mathrm{mm}$, $\dfrac{32h}{3\pi^2}=\dfrac{64}{3\pi^2}\approx2.16\ \mathrm{mm}$, so

$$ \begin{aligned} C_1&=2.16\times\sin\tfrac\pi4\approx1.53\ \mathrm{mm},\qquad C_2=\frac{2.16}{4}\sin\tfrac\pi2\approx0.540\ \mathrm{mm},\qquad C_3=\frac{2.16}{9}\sin\tfrac{3\pi}{4}\approx0.170\ \mathrm{mm},\\ C_4&=\frac{2.16}{16}\sin\pi=0,\qquad C_5=\frac{2.16}{25}\sin\tfrac{5\pi}{4}\approx-0.0611\ \mathrm{mm} \end{aligned} $$

(2) The harmonics with $n=4,8,12,\ldots$ (multiples of 4) vanish. For those $n$ with $\sin(n\pi/4)=0$, the plucked point $x_0=L/4$ coincides with a node of the corresponding normal mode $X_n(x)=\sin(n\pi x/L)$ ($X_n(L/4)=\sin(n\pi/4)=0$). Plucking a string at a node does not excite that normal mode.

(3) If the midpoint is plucked, $C_1\approx1.62\ \mathrm{mm}$, $C_2=0$, $C_3\approx-0.180\ \mathrm{mm}$, $C_4=0$, and $C_5\approx0.0648\ \mathrm{mm}$; all even-order components vanish, and the sound is a clear tone in which the fundamental accounts for almost everything. By contrast, when the point at $L/4$ is plucked, the second harmonic is present with an amplitude of about $0.540/1.53\approx35\%$ of the fundamental (it is 0 for the midpoint), and the third harmonic also remains at a comparable level. Because even-order components are included, the sound is richer in harmonics, and its timbre is sharper and harder than when the midpoint is plucked.

Exercise 17.4: Normal modes of closed and open pipes and the boundary conditions

Solution

(1) For the open pipe, $f_n=nv/(2L)$ and $v/(2L)=340/(2\times0.85)=200\ \mathrm{Hz}$, so $f_1,f_2,f_3,f_4=200,\ 400,\ 600,\ 800\ \mathrm{Hz}$. For the closed pipe, $f_n=(2n-1)v/(4L)$ and $v/(4L)=340/(4\times0.85)=100\ \mathrm{Hz}$, so $f_1,f_2,f_3,f_4=100,\ 300,\ 500,\ 700\ \mathrm{Hz}$.

(2) $300\ \mathrm{Hz}=3\times100\ \mathrm{Hz}$ coincides with the $n=2$ natural frequency of the closed pipe, so the closed pipe resonates. The natural frequencies of the open pipe are integer multiples of $200\ \mathrm{Hz}$ ($200,400,\ldots$), so $300\ \mathrm{Hz}$ is not among them.

(3) The fundamental frequency of the open pipe is $v/(2L)=200\ \mathrm{Hz}$. If the fundamental frequency $v/(4L')$ of a closed pipe of length $L'$ equals this, then $L'=v/(4\times200)=0.425\ \mathrm{m}=L/2$. To produce a note of the same pitch, the closed pipe needs only half the length of the open pipe.

(4) At the closed end $x=0$ the displacement has a node, so $X(0)=0$. Substituting into the general solution $X=B\sin kx+C\cos kx$ gives $C=0$, that is, $X=B\sin kx$. At the open end $x=L$ the displacement has an antinode ($\partial y/\partial x=0$), so $X'(L)=Bk\cos kL=0$. Since $B\ne0,\ k\ne0$, we must have $\cos kL=0$, and only the discrete values $kL=\dfrac\pi2,\ \dfrac{3\pi}2,\ \dfrac{5\pi}2,\ldots$, that is, $k_n=\dfrac{(2n-1)\pi}{2L}$ ($n=1,2,\ldots$), are allowed. Then $\lambda_n=2\pi/k_n=4L/(2n-1)$, that is, $L=(2n-1)\lambda_n/4$ ($L$ is an odd multiple of $1/4$ of the wavelength), and we obtain $f_n=vk_n/2\pi=(2n-1)v/(4L)$. It is the requirement that the boundary conditions be satisfied at the two ends simultaneously that makes $k$ discrete.

Exercise 17.5: Group velocity of water-surface waves including surface tension

Solution

(1) Differentiating both sides of $\omega^2=gk+\dfrac{\sigma}{\rho_{\mathrm m}} k^3$ with respect to $k$ gives $2\omega\dfrac{\dd\omega}{\dd k}=g+\dfrac{3\sigma}{\rho_{\mathrm m}}k^2$, so

$$ v_{\mathrm g}=\frac{\dd\omega}{\dd k}=\frac{g+3\sigma k^2/\rho_{\mathrm m}}{2\omega}=\frac{g+3\sigma k^2/\rho_{\mathrm m}}{2\sqrt{gk+\sigma k^3/\rho_{\mathrm m}}} $$
