(2) Since $v_{\mathrm p}=\omega/k$, $\dfrac{v_{\mathrm g}}{v_{\mathrm p}}=\dfrac{k\,(g+3\sigma k^2/\rho_{\mathrm m})}{2\omega^2}=\dfrac{g+3s}{2(g+s)}$ (we let $s=\sigma k^2/\rho_{\mathrm m}$ and used $\omega^2=k(g+s)$). In the long-wavelength limit ($s\ll g$) this is $\dfrac{g}{2g}=\dfrac12$, and in the short-wavelength limit ($s\gg g$) it is $\dfrac{3s}{2s}=\dfrac32$. That is, for long waves $v_{\mathrm g}=\tfrac12v_{\mathrm p}$, and for short ripples $v_{\mathrm g}=\tfrac32v_{\mathrm p}$, so the order of the two speeds is reversed.

(3) $\lambda=5.0\ \mathrm{cm}$: $k=2\pi/0.050\approx125.7\ \mathrm{rad/m}$, $\omega^2=9.8\times125.7+\dfrac{0.072}{1.0\times10^3}\times125.7^3\approx1231.5+142.9=1374.4$, and $\omega\approx37.07\ \mathrm{rad/s}$. Hence $v_{\mathrm p}=\omega/k\approx0.295\ \mathrm{m/s}$ and $v_{\mathrm g}=\dfrac{9.8+3\times7.2\times10^{-5}\times125.7^2}{2\times37.07}=\dfrac{9.8+3.41}{74.14}\approx0.178\ \mathrm{m/s}$ ($v_{\mathrm g}/v_{\mathrm p}\approx0.60$).

$\lambda=0.50\ \mathrm{cm}$: $k\approx1256.6\ \mathrm{rad/m}$, $\omega^2=9.8\times1256.6+7.2\times10^{-5}\times1256.6^3\approx1.23\times10^4+1.43\times10^5=1.55\times10^5$, and $\omega\approx394\ \mathrm{rad/s}$. $v_{\mathrm p}\approx0.313\ \mathrm{m/s}$ and $v_{\mathrm g}=\dfrac{9.8+3\times7.2\times10^{-5}\times1256.6^2}{2\times394}=\dfrac{9.8+341}{788}\approx0.445\ \mathrm{m/s}$ ($v_{\mathrm g}/v_{\mathrm p}\approx1.42$; here the group velocity is the faster).

(4) Setting $v_{\mathrm g}=v_{\mathrm p}$ in the formula from (2), $g+3s=2(g+s)$ gives $s=g$, that is, $\sigma k_{\mathrm c}^2/\rho_{\mathrm m}=g$, so

$$ k_{\mathrm c}=\sqrt{\frac{\rho_{\mathrm m} g}{\sigma}}=\sqrt{\frac{1.0\times10^3\times9.8}{0.072}}\approx369\ \mathrm{rad/m},\qquad \lambda_{\mathrm c}=\frac{2\pi}{k_{\mathrm c}}\approx1.70\ \mathrm{cm} $$

At this wavenumber, $\omega^2=k_{\mathrm c}(g+s)=2gk_{\mathrm c}$, so $v_{\mathrm p}=\omega/k_{\mathrm c}=\sqrt{2g/k_{\mathrm c}}\approx0.23\ \mathrm{m/s}$. This is the wavelength at which the phase velocity is minimum (since $v_{\mathrm g}=v_{\mathrm p}+k\,\dd v_{\mathrm p}/\dd k$, the condition $v_{\mathrm g}=v_{\mathrm p}$ is equivalent to $\dd v_{\mathrm p}/\dd k=0$); waves longer than this behave as gravity waves, and shorter ones as capillary waves (surface tension waves).

Exercise 17.6: Spreading of a swell wave packet

Solution

(1) From $\omega=\sqrt{gk}$, $\omega'=\dfrac12\sqrt g\,k^{-1/2}$ and $\omega''=-\dfrac14\sqrt g\,k^{-3/2}$. Since $k_0=2\pi/20\approx0.3142\ \mathrm{rad/m}$, we have $k_0^{-3/2}\approx5.68\ \mathrm{m^{3/2}}$ and $\sqrt g\approx3.130\ \mathrm{m^{1/2}/s}$, so

$$ \beta=\omega''(k_0)=-\frac14\times3.130\times5.68\approx-4.44\ \mathrm{m^2/s} $$

(The group velocity is $v_{\mathrm g}=\omega'(k_0)=\tfrac12\sqrt{g/k_0}\approx2.79\ \mathrm{m/s}$.)

(2) The Fourier components of $\psi(x,0)=e^{-x^2/(2\sigma_0^2)}e^{ik_0x}$ (the distribution in $q=k-k_0$) are given by $A(q)=\sigma_0\sqrt{2\pi}\,e^{-\sigma_0^2q^2/2}$. Using $\omega\approx\omega_0+v_{\mathrm g}q+\tfrac12\beta q^2$ and $s=x-v_{\mathrm g}t$, the waveform at time $t$ is

$$ \psi(x,t)=e^{i(k_0x-\omega_0t)}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int A(q)\,e^{iqs-\frac i2\beta q^2t}\,\dd q =e^{i(k_0x-\omega_0t)}\frac{\sigma_0}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int e^{-\frac12(\sigma_0^2+i\beta t)q^2+iqs}\,\dd q $$

From the Gaussian integral formula ($a=\sigma_0^2+i\beta t$, $\mathrm{Re}\,a=\sigma_0^2\gt0$), we obtain

$$ \psi(x,t)=e^{i(k_0x-\omega_0t)}\,\frac{\sigma_0}{\sqrt{\sigma_0^2+i\beta t}}\exp\!\left[-\frac{s^2}{2(\sigma_0^2+i\beta t)}\right] $$

Taking the real part of $\dfrac{1}{\sigma_0^2+i\beta t}=\dfrac{\sigma_0^2-i\beta t}{\sigma_0^4+\beta^2t^2}$, the envelope is

$$ |\psi|\propto\exp\!\left[-\frac{s^2\sigma_0^2}{2(\sigma_0^4+\beta^2t^2)}\right]=\exp\!\left[-\frac{(x-v_{\mathrm g}t)^2}{2\sigma(t)^2}\right],\qquad \sigma(t)^2=\frac{\sigma_0^4+\beta^2t^2}{\sigma_0^2}=\sigma_0^2\left[1+\left(\frac{\beta t}{\sigma_0^2}\right)^2\right] $$

Hence the envelope remains Gaussian: its center advances as $v_{\mathrm g}t$ while its width broadens to $\sigma(t)=\sigma_0\sqrt{1+(\beta t/\sigma_0^2)^2}$.

(3) $\sigma(t)=\sqrt2\,\sigma_0$ occurs when $(\beta t/\sigma_0^2)^2=1$, that is, at

$$ t=\frac{\sigma_0^2}{|\beta|}=\frac{(50\ \mathrm{m})^2}{4.44\ \mathrm{m^2/s}}\approx5.6\times10^2\ \mathrm{s}\approx9.4\ \text{min} $$

During that time the wave group travels $v_{\mathrm g}t\approx2.79\times563\approx1.6\times10^3\ \mathrm{m}=1.6\ \mathrm{km}$. (Here $\Delta k=1/\sigma_0=0.020\ \mathrm{rad/m}\ll k_0$, so the wave packet is quasi-monochromatic.) Since the group velocity $v_{\mathrm g}=\tfrac12\sqrt{g\lambda/2\pi}$ is larger for longer waves, in a swell generated by a distant storm the waves reach the shore in order, the longest first. (The width of the envelope at time $563\ \mathrm{s}$ is $70.7\ \mathrm{m}\approx\sqrt2\times50\ \mathrm{m}$.)

Exercise 17.7: The difference between the Fourier series and the Fourier transform

Solution

(1) The line spacing is $1/T$, and the first zero of the envelope is at $f=1/\tau=1/(2.0\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{s})=5.0\times10^5\ \mathrm{Hz}=500\ \mathrm{kHz}$ (independent of $T$). The line located there is the one with index $n=f\cdot T=T/\tau$, and its height is 0, so the number of nonzero lines up to the zero is $T/\tau-1$. The amplitude of the fundamental is $a_1=\dfrac{2A}{\pi}\sin\dfrac{\pi\tau}{T}$.

  • $T=8.0\ \mathrm{\mu s}$: the spacing is $1/T=125\ \mathrm{kHz}$ and $T/\tau=4$, so there are three nonzero lines, and $a_1=\dfrac{2\times1.0}{\pi}\sin\dfrac\pi4\approx0.450\ \mathrm{V}$.
  • $T=80\ \mathrm{\mu s}$: the spacing is $1/T=12.5\ \mathrm{kHz}$ and $T/\tau=40$, so there are 39 nonzero lines, and $a_1=\dfrac2\pi\sin\dfrac\pi{40}\approx0.0500\ \mathrm{V}$.

(2) As $T$ is increased, the line spacing $1/T$ approaches 0, and the number of lines increases without limit. Meanwhile the height of each line approaches 0 in proportion to $\tau/T$, but the shape of the envelope (of the form $\sin(\pi f\tau)/(\pi f\tau)$, with its first zero at $1/\tau$) does not change. As $T\to\infty$ the lines join without gaps into a continuous spectrum with the shape of the envelope. The Fourier series of a periodic pulse train thus turns into the Fourier transform of a single isolated pulse.

(3) (Sample answer) The Fourier series deals with waveforms that have a period and represents them as a sum of sinusoids at the discrete frequencies that are integer multiples of the fundamental frequency, so the spectrum is a line spectrum. The Fourier transform deals with non-periodic waveforms such as a single pulse. It is the limit in which the period is made infinite: the spacing between the lines goes to 0 so that the frequency becomes continuous, and the sum is replaced by an integral. Consequently, the spectrum is continuous. In both cases, the waveform is expressed as a superposition of sinusoids (complex exponentials).

Exercise 17.8: Width and spectrum of an ultrashort laser pulse

Solution

(1) $\Delta x=c\tau=2.998\times10^8\times100\times10^{-15}\approx3.0\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{m}=30\ \mathrm{\mu m}$.

(2) From $\Delta t\,\Delta\omega\sim2\pi$, $\Delta f=\Delta\omega/2\pi\sim1/\tau=1/(1.0\times10^{-13}\ \mathrm{s})=1.0\times10^{13}\ \mathrm{Hz}$ ($10\ \mathrm{THz}$). Differentiating $\lambda=c/f$ gives $\Delta\lambda\approx\lambda^2\Delta f/c$, so

$$ \Delta\lambda\approx\frac{(8.0\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{m})^2\times1.0\times10^{13}\ \mathrm{Hz}}{2.998\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s}}\approx2.1\times10^{-8}\ \mathrm{m}=21\ \mathrm{nm} $$

(The same value is obtained from the spatial relation $\Delta x\,\Delta k\sim2\pi$: $\Delta\lambda\approx\lambda^2/\Delta x=(8.0\times10^{-7})^2/(3.0\times10^{-5})\approx21\ \mathrm{nm}$.)

(3) The period of the light is $\lambda/c=8.0\times10^{-7}/2.998\times10^8\approx2.7\times10^{-15}\ \mathrm{s}=2.7\ \mathrm{fs}$, so the number of light waves contained in the pulse is $\tau/(\lambda/c)=100/2.67\approx37$.

(4) For $\tau=10\ \mathrm{fs}$, $\Delta x=3.0\ \mathrm{\mu m}$, $\Delta f\sim1/\tau=1.0\times10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}$, and $\Delta\lambda\approx\lambda^2\Delta f/c\approx2.1\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{m}=210\ \mathrm{nm}$ (10 times that in (2)), and the number of waves contained is about $3.7$. The shorter the pulse, the wider the spread in frequency (wavelength); since $\Delta\lambda$ reaches about one quarter of the central wavelength $800\ \mathrm{nm}$, this estimate gives only the order of magnitude.

← Back to Chapter 17, “Superposition of Waves and Fourier Analysis — Wave Packets and Group Velocity”

Chapter 18 Fundamentals of Mechanics of Materials — Loads, Stress, and Strain

Exercise 18.1: Tensile stress in a bolt

Solution

The cross-sectional area of a bolt of diameter $d_0=12\ \mathrm{mm}$ is the area of a circle of radius $d_0/2=6\ \mathrm{mm}$:

$$ A_0=\pi\left(\frac{d_0}{2}\right)^2=\pi\times(6\ \mathrm{mm})^2=\pi\times36\ \mathrm{mm^2}\approx113.10\ \mathrm{mm^2} $$

Substituting the load $P=8\ \mathrm{kN}=8\times10^3\ \mathrm N$ into the definition of stress from Chapter 18, $\sigma=P/A$, we obtain

$$ \sigma=\frac{P}{A_0}=\frac{8\times10^3\ \mathrm N}{113.10\ \mathrm{mm^2}}\approx70.7\ \mathrm{N/mm^2}=70.7\ \mathrm{MPa} $$

(we used the fact that $1\ \mathrm{N/mm^2}=1\ \mathrm{MPa}$). Giving the answer to three significant figures, $\sigma\approx70.7\ \mathrm{MPa}$.

Exercise 18.2: Transverse strain in a steel bar

Solution

From the definition of Poisson's ratio in Chapter 18, $\nu=\abs{\varepsilon'/\varepsilon}$, and the fact that when the longitudinal strain is positive (tension, $\varepsilon\gt0$) the transverse strain is negative ($\varepsilon'\lt0$), we can write $\varepsilon'=-\nu\varepsilon$. Substituting $\nu=0.30$ and $\varepsilon=2.0\times10^{-4}$, we get

$$ \varepsilon'=-\nu\varepsilon=-0.30\times2.0\times10^{-4}=-6.0\times10^{-5} $$

The magnitude of the transverse strain ($6.0\times10^{-5}$) is about 0.3 times ($=\nu$) the longitudinal strain ($2.0\times10^{-4}$), which is exactly what the definition of Poisson's ratio requires.

Exercise 18.3: Exact and approximate angles for the shear strain $\gamma=0.05$

Solution

From the definition of shear strain in Chapter 18, $\gamma=\tan\alpha$, we have $\alpha=\arctan\gamma$.

(a) The exact angle is obtained by substituting $\gamma=0.05$:

$$ \alpha_{\text{exact}}=\arctan(0.05)\approx0.049958\ \mathrm{rad}\ (\approx2.862^\circ) $$

(b) Using the small-angle approximation $\alpha\approx\gamma$, we have

$$ \alpha_{\text{approx}}\approx\gamma=0.05\ \mathrm{rad}\ (\approx2.865^\circ) $$

(c) The relative error between the two is

$$ \frac{\abs{\alpha_{\text{exact}}-\alpha_{\text{approx}}}}{\alpha_{\text{exact}}}=\frac{\abs{0.049958-0.050000}}{0.049958}\approx0.083\ \% $$

In Example 18.11 of Chapter 18 ($\gamma=0.10$) the relative error was about $0.33\ \%$, whereas in the present problem ($\gamma=0.05$) it is about $0.083\ \%$, roughly one quarter as large. This can be explained as follows. Since $\tan\alpha=\alpha+\alpha^{3}/3+\cdots$ (Taylor expansion), the relative error of the approximation $\gamma\approx\alpha$ is

$$ \frac{\tan\alpha-\alpha}{\alpha}\approx\frac{\alpha^{2}}{3} $$

When $\alpha$ is halved ($\gamma$ goes from $0.10$ to $0.05$), the relative error becomes $(1/2)^2=1/4$ of its former value, and indeed $0.33\ \%\to0.083\ \%$ agrees exactly with the calculated value. (Note that the absolute error $\tan\alpha-\alpha\approx\alpha^{3}/3$ is proportional to $\alpha^{3}$, so when $\alpha$ is halved it becomes about $1/8$.) In any case, for a small shear strain such as $\gamma=0.05$, the approximation $\alpha\approx\gamma$ can be said to be extremely accurate.

Exercise 18.4: Which basic assumption tends to break down in porous materials?

Solution

A metal foam contains a large number of pores (cavities) inside. Definition 18.1 (homogeneous body) in Chapter 18 was the assumption that “the material is a continuous solid with no defects or cavities inside, and every part has the same properties,” so for a metal foam full of pores it is the assumption of a homogeneous body that is most likely to fail. When the size of the pores becomes comparable to the dimensions of the member under consideration (for example, the thickness of a plate), the approximation that “the material has the same properties wherever we take a piece from it” no longer holds, and the positions, sizes, and arrangement of the pores themselves begin to govern the mechanical behavior.

Furthermore, when the shape or distribution of the pores is biased toward a particular direction (for example, a metal foam in which the pores have been stretched and aligned in one direction by the manufacturing process), the assumption of an isotropic body (Definition 18.2) fails at the same time—because the strength and the ease of elongation of the material become different in the direction along which the pores are lined up and in the direction perpendicular to it. Thus the two basic assumptions, homogeneous body and isotropic body, both rest on the premise that “the material can be regarded as a continuum whose structure is sufficiently fine compared with the scale of the problem under consideration,” and for a material with “a non-uniform structure of non-negligible size,” such as pores, both assumptions need to be checked carefully.

Exercise 18.5: Saint-Venant's principle and the width of a member

Solution

According to Saint-Venant's principle (Law 18.2) of Chapter 18, the disturbance that the details of how an external force is applied produce in the stress becomes small at a distance of about the representative size of the region over which that external force acts. When a concentrated load acts on the end of a flat plate, the “representative size” is the width $b$ of the plate that carries the load.

Therefore, as a general rule of thumb, in a cross section located at least about the plate width $b$ away from the point of application of the load (to be on the safe side, one to two times $b$), the stress distribution can be regarded as nearly uniform. As a numerical example, when the non-uniform stress components of a strip are examined by the theory of elasticity, it is known that the magnitude of the slowest-decaying component decreases, as a function of the distance $x$ from the load point, roughly in proportion to $e^{-4.2x/b}$. Taking its magnitude at the load point to be $1$, this is about $12\ \%$ at $x=0.5b$, about $1.5\ \%$ at $x=b$, and about $0.02\ \%$ at $x=2b$. Likewise, for the circular hole of Example 18.2, if $d$ is the diameter of the hole that produces the disturbance, the increase is about $22\ \%$ at a distance $d$ from the center of the hole, about $4\ \%$ at $2d$, and about $1.5\ \%$ at $3d$, so here too the disturbance had almost vanished at “several times the size of the region of action.” Conversely, within a distance less than the width $b$ of the point of application, the assumption that the stress distribution is uniform (the working assumption usually adopted in mechanics of materials, stated at the end of Law 18.2 in Chapter 18) may fail, and a particularly careful examination (or a detailed analysis of stress concentration such as the one we shall study in Chapter 21) becomes necessary.

Exercise 18.6: Derivation of $\varepsilon_V\approx\varepsilon+2\varepsilon'$ and the meaning of Poisson's ratio $\nu=1/2$

Solution

In the first-order approximation formula for the volumetric strain from Chapter 18, $\varepsilon_V\approx\varepsilon_x+\varepsilon_y+\varepsilon_z$ (the simple sum of the longitudinal strains in the three directions), let the strain in the longitudinal direction (the direction of the load) be $\varepsilon_x=\varepsilon$, and let the strains in the two transverse directions perpendicular to it be equal by symmetry, $\varepsilon_y=\varepsilon_z=\varepsilon'$. Then

$$ \varepsilon_V\approx\varepsilon_x+\varepsilon_y+\varepsilon_z=\varepsilon+\varepsilon'+\varepsilon'=\varepsilon+2\varepsilon' $$

and the required formula is obtained. Next, by the definition of Poisson's ratio in Chapter 18, the transverse strain under uniaxial tension ($\varepsilon\gt0$) is $\varepsilon'=-\nu\varepsilon$, so substituting this gives

$$ \varepsilon_V\approx\varepsilon+2(-\nu\varepsilon)=\varepsilon(1-2\nu) $$

an expression in terms of $\varepsilon$ and $\nu$ alone. Substituting $\nu=1/2$ into this expression,

$$ \varepsilon_V\approx\varepsilon\left(1-2\times\frac12\right)=\varepsilon(1-1)=0 $$

and we confirm that the volumetric strain is exactly $0$.

The physical meaning of this result is that “a material with Poisson's ratio $\nu=1/2$ undergoes no change at all in its total volume, no matter in which direction it is pulled or pushed.” Such a material is called incompressible. It is well known that the Poisson's ratio of natural rubber is very close to $\nu\approx0.5$ (the Application in Section 18.4), which means that rubber deforms in such a way that the thinning that occurs when it is pulled is exactly enough to keep the volume constant. Conversely, when a material with $\nu\lt1/2$ (most solids, such as metals and ceramics, fall in this category) is pulled ($\varepsilon\gt0$), we have $1-2\nu\gt0$, so $\varepsilon_V=\varepsilon(1-2\nu)\gt0$; that is, the transverse contraction alone cannot fully cancel the longitudinal elongation, and the volume increases slightly overall. The volume never decreases. For example, for $\nu=0.30$ and $\varepsilon=1.0\times10^{-3}$, the first-order approximation is $\varepsilon(1-2\nu)=4.0\times10^{-4}$, while the exact formula $\varepsilon_V=(1+\varepsilon)(1+\varepsilon')^{2}-1$ (with $\varepsilon'=-3.0\times10^{-4}$) gives $3.995\times10^{-4}$, in good agreement. The bulk modulus $K$ (defined in Chapter 19 as the constant of proportionality between pressure and volumetric strain) is a quantity that expresses this “resistance to volume change,” and it can also be confirmed in the next chapter that the limit $\nu\to1/2$ corresponds to $K\to\infty$ (the volume does not change no matter what pressure is applied).

Exercise 18.7: Shear stress in a bolt

Solution

(a) The shear plane is a circle of diameter $d=8\ \mathrm{mm}$, so its area is

$$ A=\pi\left(\frac d2\right)^2=\pi\times(4\ \mathrm{mm})^2=16\pi\ \mathrm{mm^2}\approx50.27\ \mathrm{mm^2} $$

Substituting $Q=5\ \mathrm{kN}=5.0\times10^{3}\ \mathrm N$ into the definition of shear stress from Chapter 18, $\tau=Q/A$, we obtain

$$ \tau=\frac QA=\frac{5.0\times10^{3}\ \mathrm N}{50.27\ \mathrm{mm^2}}\approx99.5\ \mathrm{N/mm^2}=99.5\ \mathrm{MPa} $$

This is the shear stress.

(b) The bolt fails when the shear stress reaches the shear strength $\tau_{\max}=300\ \mathrm{MPa}=300\ \mathrm{N/mm^2}$, so rewriting $\tau=Q/A$ as $Q=\tau A$,

$$ Q_{\max}=\tau_{\max}A=300\ \mathrm{N/mm^2}\times50.27\ \mathrm{mm^2}\approx1.508\times10^{4}\ \mathrm N\approx15.1\ \mathrm{kN} $$

The load of $5\ \mathrm{kN}$ in (a) is about $1/3$ of the failure load of $15.1\ \mathrm{kN}$ (consistent with $99.5/300\approx0.33$).

Exercise 18.8: Conversion of stress units

Solution

(a) Since $1\ \mathrm{GPa}=10^{3}\ \mathrm{MPa}=10^{9}\ \mathrm{Pa}$,

$$ 1.5\ \mathrm{GPa}=1.5\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{MPa}=1500\ \mathrm{MPa},\qquad 1.5\ \mathrm{GPa}=1.5\times10^{9}\ \mathrm{Pa} $$

(b) $101.325\ \mathrm{kPa}=101.325\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{Pa}=1.01325\times10^{5}\ \mathrm{Pa}$. Since $1\ \mathrm{MPa}=10^{6}\ \mathrm{Pa}=1\ \mathrm{N/mm^2}$,

$$ 1.01325\times10^{5}\ \mathrm{Pa}=0.101325\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{Pa}=0.101325\ \mathrm{MPa}=0.101325\ \mathrm{N/mm^2} $$

so atmospheric pressure is approximately $0.1\ \mathrm{N/mm^2}$.

(c) Rewriting $\sigma=P/A$ as $P=\sigma A$ and substituting $\sigma=120\ \mathrm{MPa}=120\ \mathrm{N/mm^2}$ and $A=250\ \mathrm{mm^2}$, we obtain

$$ P=\sigma A=120\ \mathrm{N/mm^2}\times250\ \mathrm{mm^2}=3.0\times10^{4}\ \mathrm N=30\ \mathrm{kN} $$

(the unit is $\mathrm{N/mm^2}\times\mathrm{mm^2}=\mathrm N$). Converting to $\mathrm{Pa}$, with $\sigma=1.2\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{Pa}$ and $A=2.5\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{m^2}$, we still get $P=1.2\times10^{8}\times2.5\times10^{-4}=3.0\times10^{4}\ \mathrm N$, the same result.

← Back to Chapter 18, “Fundamentals of Mechanics of Materials — Loads, Stress, and Strain”

Chapter 19 Hooke's Law, Elastic Moduli, and Material Testing

Exercise 19.1: Calculating Young's modulus

Solution

Substitute the cross-sectional area $A_0=5.0\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{m^2}$, the length $l_0=2.0\ \mathrm{m}$, the load $P=1.0\times10^{4}\ \mathrm N$, and the elongation $\lambda=2.0\ \mathrm{mm}=2.0\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{m}$ (all units have been converted to SI units) into the equation $E=l_0P/(\lambda A_0)$ of Chapter 19. Then

$$ E=\frac{l_0P}{\lambda A_0}=\frac{2.0\times1.0\times10^{4}}{2.0\times10^{-3}\times5.0\times10^{-5}}=\frac{2.0\times10^{4}}{1.0\times10^{-7}}=2.0\times10^{11}\ \mathrm{Pa}=200\ \mathrm{GPa} $$

This is the value obtained. (The stress is $\sigma=P/A_0=200\ \mathrm{MPa}$ and the strain is $\varepsilon=\lambda/l_0=1.0\times10^{-3}$, and $E=\sigma/\varepsilon$ gives the same value.) This value agrees with the typical value of Young's modulus of steel (Table 19.1 of Chapter 19, $200$–$210\ \mathrm{GPa}$), so the rod is considered to be made of steel.

Exercise 19.2: Finding $G,K$ from $E,\nu$

Solution

Substitute $E=120\ \mathrm{GPa}$ and $\nu=0.35$ into the formulas $G=E/\{2(1+\nu)\}$ and $K=E/\{3(1-2\nu)\}$ of Chapter 19. First we check that $\nu=0.35$ lies within the range $-1\lt\nu\lt1/2$ allowed for an isotropic elastic body (Section 19.3.4)—since $0.35$ is inside this range, the calculation below is physically consistent.

$$ G=\frac{E}{2(1+\nu)}=\frac{120}{2\times(1+0.35)}=\frac{120}{2.70}\approx44.4\ \mathrm{GPa} $$ $$ K=\frac{E}{3(1-2\nu)}=\frac{120}{3\times(1-0.70)}=\frac{120}{3\times0.30}=\frac{120}{0.90}\approx133.3\ \mathrm{GPa} $$

These are the values obtained. The Young's modulus used here is in nearly the same range as the typical Young's modulus of copper (Table 19.1 of Chapter 19, $110$–$128\ \mathrm{GPa}$), so the resulting values of $G,K$ also agree well with the measured values for copper ($G\approx44\ \mathrm{GPa}$ or so, $K\approx130$–$140\ \mathrm{GPa}$ or so).

Exercise 19.3: Equivalent modulus of bars connected in series

Solution

Building an analogy with the spring constant. Solving the equation $E=l_0P/(\lambda A_0)$ of Chapter 19 for $\lambda$ gives $\lambda=\dfrac{l_0}{EA_0}P$. This has the same form as the elongation of a spring, $x=F/k$ (that is, $F=kx$), and we see that $l_0/(EA_0)$ corresponds to $1/k$. In other words, we can regard a single rod as a “spring” with the following spring constant:

$$ k=\frac{EA_0}{l_0} $$

Suppose that the two rods have the same cross-sectional area $A_0$ and that each has length $l_0$ (for a total length of $2l_0$ altogether). Then the spring constants of the two rods are

$$ k_1=\frac{E_1A_0}{l_0},\qquad k_2=\frac{E_2A_0}{l_0} $$

respectively.

Using the formula for springs in series. Directly applying the series-combination formula for springs learned in high school physics, $\dfrac{1}{k_{\text{comb}}}=\dfrac{1}{k_1}+\dfrac{1}{k_2}$ (this corresponds to the fact that when two springs are connected in series, the assembly as a whole is subjected to the same load $P$ and stretches by the sum of the individual elongations $\lambda_1,\lambda_2$), we obtain

$$ \frac{1}{k_{\text{comb}}}=\frac{1}{k_1}+\frac{1}{k_2}=\frac{l_0}{E_1A_0}+\frac{l_0}{E_2A_0}=\frac{l_0}{A_0}\left(\frac{1}{E_1}+\frac{1}{E_2}\right)=\frac{l_0}{A_0}\cdot\frac{E_1+E_2}{E_1E_2} $$

Taking the reciprocal of both sides gives

$$ k_{\text{comb}}=\frac{A_0}{l_0}\cdot\frac{E_1E_2}{E_1+E_2} $$

Finding $E_{\text{comb}}$. The total length of the combined rod is $2l_0$, so the Young's modulus after combination follows from the relation $k_{\text{comb}}=E_{\text{comb}}A_0/(2l_0)$ (the same “spring-constant formula for a rod,” applied to a single rod of length $2l_0$):

$$ E_{\text{comb}}=\frac{2l_0}{A_0}k_{\text{comb}}=\frac{2l_0}{A_0}\times\frac{A_0}{l_0}\cdot\frac{E_1E_2}{E_1+E_2}=\frac{2E_1E_2}{E_1+E_2} $$

This is what we obtain. It is exactly the harmonic mean of $E_1,E_2$, namely $\dfrac{2}{1/E_1+1/E_2}$ (putting the fractions in the denominator over a common denominator gives $\dfrac{2}{1/E_1+1/E_2}=\dfrac{2E_1E_2}{E_1+E_2}$). As a numerical example, if $E_1=200\ \mathrm{GPa}$ and $E_2=70\ \mathrm{GPa}$, then

$$ E_{\text{comb}}=\frac{2\times200\times70}{200+70}=\frac{28000}{270}\approx103.7\ \mathrm{GPa} $$

which is smaller than the simple average of the two values, $135\ \mathrm{GPa}$; it is a value pulled toward the softer rod ($70\ \mathrm{GPa}$). The property familiar from springs in series, that the one with the smaller elastic modulus (the softer one) has the stronger influence, appears here as well. This is because, under the same load $P$, the softer rod stretches more.

Exercise 19.4: Why the true stress keeps increasing even after necking

Solution

At the necked minimum cross section, the true strain is defined as $\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}=\ln(A_0/A)$ (Definition 19.13 of Chapter 19), so the cross-sectional area is $A=A_0e^{-\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}}$. Since the load is $P=\sigma_{\mathrm t}A$, the nominal stress, obtained by dividing the load by the initial cross-sectional area $A_0$, can be written as

$$ \frac{P}{A_0}=\sigma_{\mathrm t}\,e^{-\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}} $$

Differentiating this with respect to $\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}$ (by the product rule) gives

$$ \frac{\dd(P/A_0)}{\dd\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}}=e^{-\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}}\left(\frac{\dd\sigma_{\mathrm t}}{\dd\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}}-\sigma_{\mathrm t}\right) $$

For Hollomon's equation $\sigma_{\mathrm t}=K_{\mathrm H}\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}^{\,n}$ we have $\dd\sigma_{\mathrm t}/\dd\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}=(n/\varepsilon_{\mathrm t})\sigma_{\mathrm t}$, so

$$ \frac{\dd(P/A_0)}{\dd\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}}=e^{-\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}}\sigma_{\mathrm t}\left(\frac{n}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}}-1\right) $$

Since $e^{-\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}}\sigma_{\mathrm t}\gt0$, the sign is determined by $n/\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}-1$. It is positive for $\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}\lt n$ ($P/A_0$ increases), $0$ at $\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}=n$ (the maximum value; the tensile strength), and negative for $\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}\gt n$ ($P/A_0$ decreases). On the other hand, the true stress $\sigma_{\mathrm t}$ itself satisfies $\dd\sigma_{\mathrm t}/\dd\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}=(n/\varepsilon_{\mathrm t})\sigma_{\mathrm t}\gt0$, so it keeps increasing with $\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}$.

A qualitative restatement. When the true strain increases slightly by $\dd\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}$, the cross-sectional area $A$ decreases by the fraction $\dd\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}$ ($\dd A/A=-\dd\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}$). Meanwhile, the true stress (the strength due to work hardening) increases by the fraction $(n/\varepsilon_{\mathrm t})\dd\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}$. The load $P=\sigma_{\mathrm t}A$ changes by the fraction given by the difference of these two effects, $(n/\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}-1)\dd\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}$. Work hardening “levels off” as $\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}$ becomes larger (that is, $n/\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}$ becomes smaller), so once $\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}\gt n$, the decrease of the cross-sectional area outweighs the increase of strength due to work hardening, and the load (and therefore $P/A_0$) decreases. This is why, even though the material itself continues to work-harden (the true stress keeps increasing), the load recorded by the testing machine, that is, the nominal stress, goes down once it has passed the tensile strength.

Numerical check ($K_{\mathrm H}=636\ \mathrm{MPa}$, $n=0.166$; these are values obtained by assuming that Hollomon's equation holds unchanged in the necked region). At $\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}=n=0.166$ (the onset of necking), $\sigma_{\mathrm t}\approx472\ \mathrm{MPa}$ and $P/A_0=\sigma_{\mathrm t}e^{-0.166}\approx400\ \mathrm{MPa}$ (the tensile strength). When the true strain of the minimum cross section has advanced to $\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}=0.30$, the true stress increases to $\sigma_{\mathrm t}\approx521\ \mathrm{MPa}$, whereas the nominal stress decreases to $P/A_0=521\times e^{-0.30}\approx386\ \mathrm{MPa}$.

Note that the relation $\sigma_{\mathrm t}=\sigma(1+\varepsilon)$, which holds only while the specimen is elongating uniformly, cannot be used after necking has begun (because the average elongation over the gauge length does not represent the deformation of the minimum cross section). For the explanation after necking, we use $\varepsilon_{\mathrm t}=\ln(A_0/A)$ of the minimum cross section, as above.

Exercise 19.5: Classification of material tests

Solution

Classifying the tests according to Definition 19.5 of Chapter 19, and adding in a few words the typical property that each test is meant to examine, we obtain the following.

Static load tests (the load is applied slowly, quasi-statically):

  • Tensile test: The specimen is pulled, and the mechanical properties in the tensile direction as a whole are examined, such as the elastic modulus (Young's modulus), the yield point and proof stress, the tensile strength, and the elongation and reduction of area.
  • Compression test: The specimen is compressed, and the elastic modulus and strength in the compression direction are examined (this is especially important for evaluating the strength of brittle materials such as concrete and ceramics, which are weak in tension but strong in compression).
  • Torsion test: A twisting moment is applied to the specimen, and the shear modulus $G$ and the strength in the shear direction (related to the mechanics of torsion treated in Chapter 20) are examined.
  • Flexural test: A beam-shaped specimen is bent, and the flexural rigidity and flexural strength are examined (this is often used especially for evaluating the strength of brittle materials from which tensile specimens are hard to make; it is related to the bending of beams in Chapter 20).
  • Hardness test: An indenter (a hard sphere or pyramid) is pressed into the surface of the material, and the degree to which the surface resists deformation (hardness) is examined. It is known to have an empirical correlation with other measures of strength, such as the tensile strength.
  • Creep test: A constant load (constant stress) is applied for a long time, and the rate of the deformation that proceeds slowly with time (creep deformation) is examined. It is important for evaluating the long-term life of components used at high temperatures (such as turbine blades).

Dynamic load tests (the load is applied suddenly or repeatedly):

  • Fatigue test: Even at a stress much smaller than the tensile strength, a material can be brought to fracture by applying the load many times over (fatigue); this test examines the relationship between the stress and the number of repetitions at which this phenomenon occurs.
  • Impact test: A large load is applied instantaneously with a hammer or the like, and the ability of the material to withstand the impact and absorb energy while deforming (toughness) and its brittleness at low temperatures (embrittlement) are examined.

Note that the creep test has in common with the other static load tests that the magnitude of the load itself is constant (quasi-static), but it differs in character from tests such as the tensile test, which can be completed with an instantaneous, short-time measurement, in that it targets “deformation that appears only after a long time” (even so, it is clearly distinguished from the dynamic load tests, in which the load is applied suddenly or repeatedly, and is classified as a static load test).

Exercise 19.6: Relative magnitudes of the Young's moduli of familiar materials (advanced problem)

Solution

Prediction: reasoning from the type of bonding. As we saw in “Application: The atomistic origin of elastic moduli” in Section 19.5, Young's modulus reflects the curvature at the bottom of the interatomic potential (the strength of the bonding itself). Considering how each of the four materials resists deformation,

  • Rubber: It has a structure in which long chain molecules of hydrocarbons (polymers) are loosely entangled with one another. Most of the deformation when it is pulled does not come from stretching the individual covalent bonds themselves, but from the tangled chains unraveling and extending (a phenomenon called entropic elasticity, which has a statistical-mechanical origin—when rubber is stretched, the “disorder” of the chain configurations decreases, so it tends to return to its original state). Since the bonds themselves are not directly stretched, Young's modulus is extremely small.
  • Wood (along the grain): Cellulose molecules are aligned in a fibrous manner, and strong covalent bonds act along the direction of the fibers. When wood is pulled along the fibers, these covalent bonds are stretched directly, so it is much stiffer than rubber, but the bonds are neither as dense nor as uniform as in the steel and glass discussed below (there are also parts where the fibers are connected to one another by weak bonds such as hydrogen bonds), so it has an intermediate Young's modulus.
  • Glass (soda-lime glass): Silicate (mainly $\mathrm{SiO_2}$) forms a random network structure dominated by covalent bonds. Because strongly directional covalent bonds extend throughout, it is stiffer than wood. However, the atoms in the network are loosely packed, and the network can also deform through changes in the $\mathrm{Si\text{–}O\text{–}Si}$ angle, so it does not become as stiff as steel.
  • Steel: Metallic bonding (bonding in which metal ions are immersed in a “sea” of free electrons), with the atoms densely packed in a crystal lattice. The strength of the bonding itself is not as great as that of strong covalent bonds, but the atoms are arranged regularly at high density and all of the bonds are stretched directly. Consequently its Young's modulus is larger than that of glass, which has a network structure with many gaps.

Therefore, if we assume that Young's modulus is larger the more the “deformation that directly stretches the bonds” dominates, the predicted order is

$$ \text{rubber}\ (E\sim0.001\text{–}0.1\ \mathrm{GPa}) \ \lt\ \text{wood (along the grain)}\ (E\sim10\text{–}16\ \mathrm{GPa}) \ \lt\ \text{glass}\ (E\sim65\text{–}72\ \mathrm{GPa}) \ \lt\ \text{steel}\ (E\sim200\text{–}210\ \mathrm{GPa}) $$

This is the predicted order.

Comparison with literature values. If we line up the values for steel ($200$–$210\ \mathrm{GPa}$) and soda-lime glass ($65$–$72\ \mathrm{GPa}$) listed in Table 19.1 of Chapter 19 together with the typical values found in general engineering-materials data collections—for wood (along the grain, with a range depending on the species), $E\approx10$–$16\ \mathrm{GPa}$ or so, and for rubber, a few $\mathrm{MPa}$ for soft types and about $0.1\ \mathrm{GPa}$ for hard types (with a wide range depending on the degree of vulcanization)—we can confirm that they agree well with the order predicted above (rubber ≪ wood < glass < steel). That the Young's moduli are spread over more than four orders of magnitude (the ratio of steel to rubber is roughly $10^{3}$–$10^{5}$) is an example that shows plainly how great an influence the atomistic differences in the “type and density of the bonds” have on the macroscopic mechanical properties.

← Back to Chapter 19, “Hooke's Law, Elastic Moduli, and Material Testing”

Chapter 20 Multiaxial Stress, Bending of Beams, and Torsion

Exercise 20.1: Principal stresses and Mohr's circle

Solution

We have $\sigma_x=-40\ \mathrm{MPa}$, $\sigma_y=80\ \mathrm{MPa}$, and $\tau_{xy}=-25\ \mathrm{MPa}$. Computing the mean and the half-difference, we obtain

$$ \frac{\sigma_x+\sigma_y}2=\frac{-40+80}2=20\ \mathrm{MPa},\qquad \frac{\sigma_x-\sigma_y}2=\frac{-40-80}2=-60\ \mathrm{MPa} $$

(1) From the principal stress formula of Chapter 20 (Law 20.2), $\sigma_{1,2}=\dfrac{\sigma_x+\sigma_y}2\pm\sqrt{\left(\dfrac{\sigma_x-\sigma_y}2\right)^2+\tau_{xy}^2}$, the square-root term is $\sqrt{(-60)^2+(-25)^2}=\sqrt{3600+625}=\sqrt{4225}=65\ \mathrm{MPa}$, so

$$ \sigma_1=20+65=85\ \mathrm{MPa},\qquad \sigma_2=20-65=-45\ \mathrm{MPa} $$

The maximum in-plane shear stress, being half the difference of the principal stresses (or the value of the square root itself), is $\tau_{\max}=65\ \mathrm{MPa}$ (since $\sigma_1,\sigma_2$ have opposite signs, this is also the three-dimensional absolute maximum shear stress $(\sigma_1-\sigma_2)/2$).

(2) The center of Mohr's circle is $\left(\dfrac{\sigma_x+\sigma_y}2,0\right)=(20,0)\ \mathrm{MPa}$, and the radius is $R=65\ \mathrm{MPa}$, the same as the value of the square root found above. The points where the circle meets the $\sigma$ axis are $20\pm65$, that is, $85\ \mathrm{MPa}$ on the right and $-45\ \mathrm{MPa}$ on the left, which agree exactly with the $\sigma_1,\sigma_2$ found in (1). The $\tau$ coordinates of the top and bottom of the circle are also $\pm65\ \mathrm{MPa}$, which agree with $\tau_{\max}$. We have thus confirmed that the graphical method (Mohr's circle) and the algebraic method (the eigenvalue computation) arrive at the same answer (confirmation by another method: solving the characteristic equation $\det\begin{pmatrix}-40-\lambda&-25\\-25&80-\lambda\end{pmatrix}=\lambda^2-40\lambda-3825=0$ directly gives $\lambda=(40\pm\sqrt{1600+15300})/2=(40\pm130)/2=85,\,-45$, which agrees.)

Exercise 20.2: Thermal stress in a constrained aluminum bar

Solution

Substituting $E=70\ \mathrm{GPa}$, $\alpha=2.3\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{K^{-1}}$, and $\Delta T=-30\ \mathrm K$ into the formula $\sigma=-E\alpha\Delta T$ for the thermal stress in a bar whose two ends are fixed to rigid walls (Formula 20.2 of Chapter 20), we obtain

$$ \sigma=-E\alpha\Delta T=-(70\times10^9\ \mathrm{Pa})\times(2.3\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{K^{-1}})\times(-30\ \mathrm K) $$

Paying attention to the signs in the computation, we have $(-1)\times(-30)=+30$, so

$$ \sigma=70\times10^9\times2.3\times10^{-5}\times30\ \mathrm{Pa}=70\times2.3\times30\times10^4\ \mathrm{Pa}=4830\times10^4\ \mathrm{Pa}=4.83\times10^7\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx48.3\ \mathrm{MPa} $$

Since the sign is positive, this is a tensile stress. This agrees with the physical explanation: when the temperature falls ($\Delta T\lt0$), the bar would naturally contract, but the walls at both ends do not allow it, so a stress in the direction that stretches the bar (tension) arises.

Exercise 20.3: Bending stress in a beam with a circular cross section

Solution

From the formula $I=\pi d^4/64$ for a circular cross section in Chapter 20, the second moment of area of a circular cross section of diameter $d=60\ \mathrm{mm}=0.060\ \mathrm m$ is

$$ I=\frac{\pi\times(0.060\ \mathrm m)^4}{64}=\frac{\pi\times1.296\times10^{-5}}{64}\ \mathrm{m^4}\approx6.362\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{m^4} $$

The distance from the neutral axis to the edge is $y_{\max}=d/2=0.030\ \mathrm m$, so from the bending stress formula $\sigma=My/I$,

$$ \sigma_{\max}=\frac{M\,y_{\max}}I=\frac{3.0\times10^3\ \mathrm{N\cdot m}\times0.030\ \mathrm m}{6.362\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{m^4}}=\frac{90}{6.362\times10^{-7}}\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx1.415\times10^8\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx141.5\ \mathrm{MPa} $$

(unit check: $[\mathrm{N\cdot m\cdot m/m^4}]=[\mathrm{N/m^2}]=[\mathrm{Pa}]$ is correct. Using the section modulus $Z=\pi d^3/32=\pi\times(0.060)^3/32\approx2.121\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{m^3}$, the same value follows immediately: $\sigma_{\max}=M/Z=3.0\times10^3/2.121\times10^{-5}\approx1.415\times10^8\ \mathrm{Pa}$.) The procedure is the same as in the example with a rectangular cross section (Example 20.8 of Chapter 20); note that only the formula for the second moment of area differs.

Exercise 20.4: Derivation of the deflection curve (right half) of a simply supported beam

Solution

In the interval $L/2\le x\le L$, by Section 20.6.2 of Chapter 20, $M(x)=\dfrac{P(L-x)}2$. We integrate both sides of the differential equation of deflection $EIv''=M(x)$ with respect to $x$. Noting that $\dd(L-x)/\dd x=-1$, and that $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd x}\left[-\dfrac P4(L-x)^2\right]=-\dfrac P4\times2(L-x)\times(-1)=\dfrac P2(L-x)=M(x)$, we see that the result of the first integration is

$$ EIv'=-\frac P4(L-x)^2+C_3 $$

(this can be checked by differentiating the right-hand side with respect to $x$, which indeed returns $M(x)$). Integrating once more in the same way, and using $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd x}\left[\dfrac P{12}(L-x)^3\right]=\dfrac P{12}\times3(L-x)^2\times(-1)=-\dfrac P4(L-x)^2$, we obtain

$$ EIv=\frac P{12}(L-x)^3+C_3x+C_4 $$

We determine the constants of integration $C_3,C_4$ from the following two conditions.

(a) Slope condition: At the interval boundary $x=L/2$, the slope in the right interval must agree with the value at $x=L/2$ of the slope in the left interval, $EIv'=\dfrac P4x^2-\dfrac{PL^2}{16}$, namely $EIv'(L/2)=\dfrac P4\left(\dfrac L2\right)^2-\dfrac{PL^2}{16}=\dfrac{PL^2}{16}-\dfrac{PL^2}{16}=0$ (by symmetry, the bottom of the deflection curve):

$$ -\frac P4\left(\frac L2\right)^2+C_3=0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad -\frac{PL^2}{16}+C_3=0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad C_3=\frac{PL^2}{16} $$

(b) Boundary condition at the support: At the right end $x=L$, the deflection at the support must be 0:

$$ EIv(L)=\frac P{12}(L-L)^3+C_3L+C_4=0+\frac{PL^2}{16}\cdot L+C_4=\frac{PL^3}{16}+C_4=0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad C_4=-\frac{PL^3}{16} $$

Therefore, the deflection curve in the interval $L/2\le x\le L$ is

$$ EIv(x)=\frac P{12}(L-x)^3+\frac{PL^2}{16}x-\frac{PL^3}{16}\qquad\left(\frac L2\le x\le L\right) $$

(expanding with $(L-x)^3=L^3-3L^2x+3Lx^2-x^3$, it can also be put in the form $EIv(x)=\dfrac{PL^3}{12}-\dfrac{PL^2}{4}x+\dfrac{PL}4x^2-\dfrac P{12}x^3+\dfrac{PL^2}{16}x-\dfrac{PL^3}{16}=\dfrac{PL^3}{48}-\dfrac{3PL^2}{16}x+\dfrac{PL}4x^2-\dfrac P{12}x^3$).

(c) Let us use the constants of integration just determined to check that the deflection at $x=L/2$ agrees with the result for the left interval. Substituting $x=L/2$, we obtain

$$ EIv\left(\frac L2\right)=\frac P{12}\left(\frac L2\right)^3+\frac{PL^2}{16}\cdot\frac L2-\frac{PL^3}{16}=\frac{PL^3}{96}+\frac{PL^3}{32}-\frac{PL^3}{16} $$

Putting the terms over a common denominator (96), we obtain

$$ \frac{PL^3}{96}+\frac{3PL^3}{96}-\frac{6PL^3}{96}=\frac{(1+3-6)PL^3}{96}=-\frac{2PL^3}{96}=-\frac{PL^3}{48} $$

That is, $v(L/2)=-\dfrac{PL^3}{48EI}$, which indeed agrees exactly with the value of the maximum deflection $\delta_{\max}=PL^3/(48EI)$ obtained from the left interval in Section 20.6.4, apart from the sign ($v$ is positive upward, so a downward deflection is negative). Although the constants of integration were determined from the two conditions (a) and (b) alone, the continuity of the deflection itself was also satisfied automatically; this confirms that, starting from either the left or the right interval, we arrive without contradiction at the same single deflection curve and the same maximum deflection.

Exercise 20.5: Designing a transmission shaft

Solution

(1) From the rotational speed $N=1200\ \mathrm{rpm}$, the angular velocity is

$$ \omega=\frac{2\pi N}{60}=\frac{2\pi\times1200}{60}\ \mathrm{rad/s}=2\pi\times20\ \mathrm{rad/s}\approx125.66\ \mathrm{rad/s} $$

From the power formula $P=T\omega$, the required torque is

$$ T=\frac P\omega=\frac{45\times10^3\ \mathrm W}{125.66\ \mathrm{rad/s}}\approx358.1\ \mathrm{N\cdot m} $$

As in Example 20.15 of Chapter 20, we substitute $J=\pi d^4/32$ for a circular cross section and $r=d/2$ into the torsional stress formula $\tau_{\max}=Tr/J$ and simplify, which gives the relation $\tau_{\max}=16T/(\pi d^3)$; substituting $\tau_{\max}=\tau_{\mathrm a}=35\ \mathrm{MPa}$ into this relation and solving for $d$, we obtain

$$ d=\left(\frac{16T}{\pi\tau_{\mathrm a}}\right)^{1/3}=\left(\frac{16\times358.1}{\pi\times35\times10^6}\right)^{1/3}\ \mathrm m=\left(\frac{5730}{1.0996\times10^8}\right)^{1/3}\ \mathrm m\approx\left(5.212\times10^{-5}\right)^{1/3}\ \mathrm m\approx0.03735\ \mathrm m $$

That is, $d\approx37.4\ \mathrm{mm}$. As in Example 20.15, in practice it is customary to choose a standard diameter at least this large (for example $d=40\ \mathrm{mm}$).

(2) The polar second moment of area of a shaft with $d=40\ \mathrm{mm}=0.040\ \mathrm m$ is $J=\dfrac{\pi d^4}{32}=\dfrac{\pi\times(0.040)^4}{32}\ \mathrm{m^4}\approx2.513\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{m^4}$. The maximum shear stress at the surface is

$$ \tau_{\max}=\frac{16T}{\pi d^3}=\frac{16\times358.1}{\pi\times(0.040)^3}\ \mathrm{Pa}=\frac{5730}{2.011\times10^{-4}}\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx2.85\times10^7\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx28.5\ \mathrm{MPa} $$

which is smaller than the allowable stress of $35\ \mathrm{MPa}$, so there is a margin. For the angle of twist, with $G=79\ \mathrm{GPa}$ and $L=1.5\ \mathrm m$, the formula $\varphi=TL/(GJ)$ gives

$$ \varphi=\frac{358.1\times1.5}{79\times10^9\times2.513\times10^{-7}}\ \mathrm{rad}=\frac{537.2}{1.985\times10^4}\ \mathrm{rad}\approx0.02706\ \mathrm{rad} $$

Converted to degrees, this is $0.02706\times180/\pi\approx1.55°$.

Exercise 20.6: Pure shear at the shaft surface and the principal stresses in the $45°$ direction

Solution

Substituting the pure shear state $\sigma_x=\sigma_y=0$, $\tau_{xy}=\tau_{\max}$ into the principal stress formula of Chapter 20 (Law 20.2)

$$ \sigma_{1,2}=\frac{\sigma_x+\sigma_y}2\pm\sqrt{\left(\frac{\sigma_x-\sigma_y}2\right)^2+\tau_{xy}^2} $$

we find that, since $\sigma_x=\sigma_y=0$, the mean-value term is 0, and the term $\left(\dfrac{\sigma_x-\sigma_y}2\right)^2$ under the square root is also 0, so

$$ \sigma_{1,2}=0\pm\sqrt{0+\tau_{\max}^2}=\pm\tau_{\max} $$

That is, (1) $\sigma_1=+\tau_{\max}$ (tension) and $\sigma_2=-\tau_{\max}$ (compression).

(2) The orientation of the principal planes is determined by the equation $\tan2\theta_{\mathrm p}=\dfrac{2\tau_{xy}}{\sigma_x-\sigma_y}$ of Chapter 20, but since the denominator $\sigma_x-\sigma_y=0$, this equation refers to the angle for which $\tan2\theta_{\mathrm p}\to\infty$ ($2\theta_{\mathrm p}=90°$). The tangent becomes infinite when the angle is $90°$ (or an angle shifted from it by an integer multiple of that angle), so $2\theta_{\mathrm p}=90°$, that is,

$$ \theta_{\mathrm p}=45° $$

In this case $a=(\sigma_x-\sigma_y)/2=0,\ b=\tau_{\max}$, which corresponds to the sign combination $(\cos2\theta_{\mathrm p},\sin2\theta_{\mathrm p})=(a,b)/r=(0,1)$, so the plane with $\theta_{\mathrm p}=45°$ is the plane of the maximum principal stress $\sigma_1=+\tau_{\max}$. Indeed, substituting $\theta=45°$ ($\cos90°=0,\ \sin90°=1$) into the coordinate transformation formula (Formula 20.1 of Chapter 20) to check, we obtain

$$ \sigma_{x'}=0+0\times0+\tau_{\max}\times1=\tau_{\max},\qquad \tau_{x'y'}=-0\times1+\tau_{\max}\times0=0 $$

so we confirm that on the plane $\theta=45°$ the shear stress is indeed 0 and the normal stress is $\tau_{\max}$ ($=\sigma_1$).

This result is the very basis of the fact stated in Section 20.7, “Application: Principal Stresses in Torsion and the Fracture Surface of a Brittle Material”—that when a shaft with a circular cross section is subjected to torsion, its surface is in a state of pure shear, and a principal stress (the maximum tensile stress) arises in the direction inclined at $45°$ to the axial direction. When a brittle material (such as chalk or cast iron) is twisted, it fractures on the surface perpendicular to this tensile principal stress in the $45°$ direction, that is, on a helical surface inclined at $45°$ to the axis, precisely because of this calculation result.

Exercise 20.7: The generalized Hooke's law — strains in an aluminum plate

Solution

We substitute $E=70\,000\ \mathrm{MPa}$, $\nu=0.33$, $\sigma_x=120\ \mathrm{MPa}$, $\sigma_y=-40\ \mathrm{MPa}$, and $\sigma_z=0$ into the generalized Hooke's law of Chapter 20 (Law 20.3).

(1)

$$ \begin{aligned} \varepsilon_x&=\frac{\sigma_x-\nu(\sigma_y+\sigma_z)}E=\frac{120-0.33\times(-40)}{70\,000}=\frac{133.2}{70\,000}\approx1.90\times10^{-3}\\ \varepsilon_y&=\frac{\sigma_y-\nu(\sigma_z+\sigma_x)}E=\frac{-40-0.33\times120}{70\,000}=\frac{-79.6}{70\,000}\approx-1.14\times10^{-3}\\ \varepsilon_z&=\frac{\sigma_z-\nu(\sigma_x+\sigma_y)}E=\frac{0-0.33\times(120-40)}{70\,000}=\frac{-26.4}{70\,000}\approx-3.77\times10^{-4} \end{aligned} $$

Even though $\sigma_z=0$, owing to the Poisson effect of the in-plane stresses a strain $\varepsilon_z=-3.77\times10^{-4}$ (contraction) remains.

(2) The volumetric strain is the sum of the three strains: $\varepsilon_V=1.903\times10^{-3}-1.137\times10^{-3}-0.377\times10^{-3}\approx3.89\times10^{-4}$. Checking with the formula $\varepsilon_V=(1-2\nu)(\sigma_x+\sigma_y+\sigma_z)/E$ of Chapter 20, we get $(1-2\times0.33)\times(120-40)/70\,000=0.34\times80/70\,000\approx3.89\times10^{-4}$, which agrees.

(3) The change in thickness is $\Delta t=\varepsilon_zt=-3.77\times10^{-4}\times5.0\ \mathrm{mm}\approx-1.89\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{mm}$; that is, the plate becomes thinner by about $1.9\ \mathrm{\mu m}$.

Exercise 20.8: Reactions, bending moment diagram, maximum bending stress, and tip deflection of a cantilever beam

Solution

(1) From the equilibrium of forces, the reaction force at the wall is upward, $R_0=P=2.0\ \mathrm{kN}$. From the equilibrium of moments about the wall, the reaction moment is counterclockwise, $M_0=PL=2.0\times10^3\times1.5\ \mathrm{N\cdot m}=3.0\ \mathrm{kN\cdot m}$.

(2) Taking the fixed end as $x=0$ and counting the forces on the left side of the section, we have $Q(x)=R_0=2.0\ \mathrm{kN}$ (constant over the entire length) and $M(x)=R_0x-M_0=2.0x-3.0\ \mathrm{kN\cdot m}=-2.0\,(1.5-x)\ \mathrm{kN\cdot m}$ ($x$ in $\mathrm m$). We have $M(0)=-3.0\ \mathrm{kN\cdot m}$ and $M(L)=0$, and both satisfy $\dd M/\dd x=2.0=Q$.

(3) The magnitude of the bending moment is largest at the fixed end, $\abs{M}_{\max}=3.0\ \mathrm{kN\cdot m}$. The second moment of area is $I=\dfrac{bh^3}{12}=\dfrac{0.030\times0.060^3}{12}\ \mathrm{m^4}=5.4\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{m^4}$ and $y_{\max}=h/2=0.030\ \mathrm m$, so

$$ \sigma_{\max}=\frac{\abs{M}_{\max}\,y_{\max}}I=\frac{3.0\times10^3\times0.030}{5.4\times10^{-7}}\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx1.667\times10^8\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx166.7\ \mathrm{MPa} $$

Since $M\lt0$ (the beam bends convex upward), the top surface of the cross section at the fixed end is in tension ($+167\ \mathrm{MPa}$) and the bottom surface of the same cross section is in compression ($-167\ \mathrm{MPa}$). This is smaller than a typical value of the yield stress of steel (about $250\ \mathrm{MPa}$).

(4) From the result $\delta_{\max}=\dfrac{PL^3}{3EI}$ of the derivation in Section 20.6.4 of Chapter 20 (cantilever beam with a concentrated load at the tip), the deflection at the tip is

$$ \delta_{\max}=\frac{2.0\times10^3\times1.5^3}{3\times206\times10^9\times5.4\times10^{-7}}\ \mathrm m=\frac{6750}{3.337\times10^5}\ \mathrm m\approx2.02\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm m\approx20.2\ \mathrm{mm} $$

That is, about $20\ \mathrm{mm}$ downward (about $1/74$ of the length).

Exercise 20.9: Four-point flexural strength

Solution

(1) The bending moment between the loading points is $M_{\max}=\dfrac P2\times\dfrac{L-L_{\mathrm i}}2=\dfrac{P(L-L_{\mathrm i})}4$. Substituting $P=960\ \mathrm N$ and $L-L_{\mathrm i}=40-20=20\ \mathrm{mm}=0.020\ \mathrm m$, we obtain

$$ M_{\max}=\frac{960\times0.020}4\ \mathrm{N\cdot m}=4.8\ \mathrm{N\cdot m} $$

The section modulus is $Z=\dfrac{bd^2}6=\dfrac{0.0040\times(0.0030)^2}6\ \mathrm{m^3}=6.0\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm{m^3}$, so the bending strength is

$$ \sigma_{\mathrm f}=\frac{M_{\max}}Z=\frac{4.8}{6.0\times10^{-9}}\ \mathrm{Pa}=8.0\times10^8\ \mathrm{Pa}=800\ \mathrm{MPa} $$

(This also agrees with the formula $\sigma_{\mathrm f}=3P(L-L_{\mathrm i})/(2bd^2)=3\times960\times0.020/(2\times0.0040\times9.0\times10^{-6})=57.6/(7.2\times10^{-8})=8.0\times10^8\ \mathrm{Pa}$ of Chapter 20.)

(2) If the same load $960\ \mathrm N$ is applied in three-point bending with span $L=40\ \mathrm{mm}$, the maximum bending moment is $M_{\max}=PL/4=960\times0.040/4=9.6\ \mathrm{N\cdot m}$, which is twice the $4.8\ \mathrm{N\cdot m}$ of four-point bending (since $L-L_{\mathrm i}=L/2$, $PL/4\div\{P(L/2)/4\}=2$). The maximum bending stress in this case is as large as $9.6/(6.0\times10^{-9})=1.6\times10^9\ \mathrm{Pa}=1600\ \mathrm{MPa}$. In other words, under the same load, in three-point bending a large stress is concentrated at the single point directly beneath the loading point, whereas in four-point bending a stress of half that magnitude acts uniformly over a wide interval between the loading points.

Exercise 20.10: Torque capacity of hollow and solid shafts

Solution

The polar second moment of area of the hollow shaft is

$$ J=\frac{\pi(D^4-d^4)}{32}=\frac{\pi\times\left\{(0.060)^4-(0.040)^4\right\}}{32}\ \mathrm{m^4}=\frac{\pi\times(1.296\times10^{-5}-2.56\times10^{-6})}{32}\ \mathrm{m^4}=\frac{\pi\times1.04\times10^{-5}}{32}\ \mathrm{m^4}\approx1.021\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{m^4} $$

The radius of the surface is $D/2=0.030\ \mathrm m$. From $\tau_{\max}=TR/J=\tau_{\mathrm a}$, the allowable torque is

$$ T_{\text{hollow}}=\frac{\tau_{\mathrm a}J}{R}=\frac{40\times10^6\times1.021\times10^{-6}}{0.030}\ \mathrm{N\cdot m}\approx1.361\times10^3\ \mathrm{N\cdot m} $$

Next, consider a solid shaft with the same cross-sectional area. The cross-sectional area of the hollow shaft is $A=\dfrac{\pi(D^2-d^2)}4=\dfrac{\pi(0.060^2-0.040^2)}4\ \mathrm{m^2}=\dfrac{\pi\times2.0\times10^{-3}}4\ \mathrm{m^2}\approx1.571\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{m^2}$. The diameter $d_{\mathrm s}$ of the solid shaft satisfies $\dfrac{\pi d_{\mathrm s}^2}4=A$, so $d_{\mathrm s}^2=D^2-d^2=2.0\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{m^2}$, that is, $d_{\mathrm s}=\sqrt{2.0\times10^{-3}}\ \mathrm m\approx0.04472\ \mathrm m=44.7\ \mathrm{mm}$. The polar second moment of area in this case is $J_{\mathrm s}=\dfrac{\pi d_{\mathrm s}^4}{32}=\dfrac{\pi\times(2.0\times10^{-3})^2}{32}\ \mathrm{m^4}\approx3.927\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{m^4}$ and the radius of the surface is $d_{\mathrm s}/2=0.02236\ \mathrm m$, so the allowable torque is

$$ T_{\text{solid}}=\frac{\tau_{\mathrm a}J_{\mathrm s}}{d_{\mathrm s}/2}=\frac{40\times10^6\times3.927\times10^{-7}}{0.02236}\ \mathrm{N\cdot m}\approx7.03\times10^2\ \mathrm{N\cdot m} $$

Therefore, $T_{\text{hollow}}/T_{\text{solid}}\approx1361/703\approx1.94$, and for the same mass, the hollow shaft can transmit about 1.9 times the torque of the solid shaft. This is because the torsional stress increases with distance from the center, and the material near the center does almost no work, so it is more efficient to move that material outward.

← Back to Chapter 20, “Multiaxial Stress, Bending of Beams, and Torsion”

Chapter 21 Fundamentals of Fracture Mechanics — Stress Concentration, Griffith Theory, and Fracture Toughness

Exercise 21.1: Deriving the stress concentration factor of a circular hole from the radius-of-curvature formula

Solution

In Chapter 21 we showed that the radius of curvature at the end of the major axis of an elliptical hole (major axis $2a$, minor axis $2b$) is

$$ \rho=\frac{b^2}{a} $$

and that, using this, Inglis's formula for the stress concentration can be written as

$$ \sigma_{\max}=\sigma\left(1+2\sqrt{\frac a\rho}\right) $$

A circular hole is the special case of an ellipse in which the major and minor axes are equal, that is, $b=a$. In this case the radius of curvature becomes

$$ \rho=\frac{b^2}{a}=\frac{a^2}{a}=a $$

Thus, for a circular hole the radius of curvature $\rho$ is simply equal to the radius $a$ (the radius of curvature of a circle is the radius of that circle itself, so this is also a natural result geometrically). Substituting this into $\sigma_{\max}=\sigma(1+2\sqrt{a/\rho})$ gives

$$ \sigma_{\max}=\sigma\left(1+2\sqrt{\frac a a}\right)=\sigma(1+2\sqrt1)=\sigma(1+2\times1)=3\sigma $$

and we indeed obtain the stress concentration factor of 3. This agrees exactly with the result obtained by substituting $a=b$ directly into Inglis's general formula $\sigma_{\max}=\sigma(1+2a/b)$ (Chapter 21, Example 21.1)—we have confirmed that the route through the radius of curvature and the route that uses the major and minor axes directly arrive at the same conclusion.

Exercise 21.2: Back-calculating the critical crack length from Griffith's criterion

Solution

Griffith's criterion (plane stress) derived in Chapter 21 is

$$ \sigma_{\mathrm f}=\sqrt{\frac{2E\gamma_{\mathrm s}}{\pi a}} $$

We solve this for the crack half-length $a$. Squaring both sides gives

$$ \sigma_{\mathrm f}^2=\frac{2E\gamma_{\mathrm s}}{\pi a} $$

Multiplying both sides by $\pi a$ and dividing by $\sigma_{\mathrm f}^2$, we obtain

$$ a=\frac{2E\gamma_{\mathrm s}}{\pi\sigma_{\mathrm f}^2} $$

Here we substitute the values for soda-lime glass, $E=70\ \mathrm{GPa}=70\times10^9\ \mathrm{Pa}$ and $\gamma_{\mathrm s}=0.6\ \mathrm{J/m^2}$, and the measured fracture stress $\sigma_{\mathrm f}=60\ \mathrm{MPa}=60\times10^6\ \mathrm{Pa}$:

$$ a=\frac{2\times70\times10^9\times0.6}{\pi\times(60\times10^6)^2}\ \mathrm m =\frac{8.4\times10^{10}}{\pi\times3.6\times10^{15}}\ \mathrm m =\frac{8.4\times10^{10}}{1.131\times10^{16}}\ \mathrm m \approx7.4\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm m $$

Unit check: the numerator is $[\mathrm{Pa\cdot J/m^2}]=[\mathrm{Pa\cdot Pa\cdot m}]=[\mathrm{Pa^2\cdot m}]$ and the denominator is $[\mathrm{Pa^2}]$, so the quotient is in $[\mathrm m]$, which is correct. Therefore, this specimen is estimated to have contained a crack of half-length $a\approx7.4\ \mu\mathrm m$ (a total length of about $15\ \mu\mathrm m$). This is a typical numerical example showing that even a tiny flaw that is almost invisible to the naked eye can greatly affect the strength of glass.

Exercise 21.3: Finding the allowable design stress

Solution

The defining equation of the stress intensity factor is

$$ K_{\mathrm I}=Y\sigma\sqrt{\pi a} $$

The condition for a crack to begin to extend unstably is $K_{\mathrm I}=K_{\mathrm{IC}}$, so we take this as the allowable limit and solve for $\sigma$:

$$ \sigma_{\text{allow}}=\frac{K_{\mathrm{IC}}}{Y\sqrt{\pi a}} $$

We substitute $K_{\mathrm{IC}}=30\ \mathrm{MPa\,m^{1/2}}=30\times10^6\ \mathrm{Pa\,m^{1/2}}$ for the aluminum alloy, the flaw depth $a=2\ \mathrm{mm}=2\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm m$, and the geometry factor $Y=1.12$ (surface crack):

$$ \sigma_{\text{allow}}=\frac{30\times10^6}{1.12\times\sqrt{\pi\times2\times10^{-3}}}\ \mathrm{Pa} =\frac{30\times10^6}{1.12\times0.0793}\ \mathrm{Pa} =\frac{30\times10^6}{0.0888}\ \mathrm{Pa} \approx3.38\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{Pa} $$

Therefore, the allowable tensile stress is approximately $338\ \mathrm{MPa}$. Unit check: dividing $K_{\mathrm{IC}}$ ($\mathrm{Pa\cdot m^{1/2}}$) by $\sqrt{\pi a}$ ($\mathrm{m^{1/2}}$) gives $\mathrm{Pa}$, which is correct. Note also that, compared with the representative yield stress of common aluminum alloys (it depends on the type of alloy and the heat treatment, but is roughly 200–300 MPa), this allowable stress of 338 MPa exceeds it, so in practice the member may yield before the crack extends. In design, one must calculate both the allowable stress from fracture mechanics and the allowable stress based on the yield strength, and adopt the smaller one.

Exercise 21.4: Judging whether the specimen thickness satisfies the plane-strain condition

Solution

The plane-strain condition of ASTM E399, as we saw in Chapter 21, is

$$ B,\ a,\ (W-a)\ \ge\ 2.5\left(\frac{K_{\mathrm{IC}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm y}}\right)^2 $$

For the thickness $B$, we calculate the minimum thickness required by the right-hand side. Substituting $K_{\mathrm{IC}}=50\ \mathrm{MPa\,m^{1/2}}=50\times10^6\ \mathrm{Pa\,m^{1/2}}$ and $\sigma_{\mathrm y}=900\ \mathrm{MPa}=900\times10^6\ \mathrm{Pa}$, we obtain

$$ B_{\min}=2.5\left(\frac{50\times10^6}{900\times10^6}\right)^2\mathrm m =2.5\times(0.0556)^2\ \mathrm m =2.5\times3.086\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm m \approx7.7\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm m=7.7\ \mathrm{mm} $$

The minimum required thickness is approximately $7.7\ \mathrm{mm}$. By contrast, the thickness of the specimen actually used is $B=6\ \mathrm{mm}$, and since $6\ \mathrm{mm}\lt7.7\ \mathrm{mm}$, this condition is not satisfied. In other words, this specimen is too thin to satisfy the plane-strain condition.

If this condition is not satisfied, the plastic zone at the crack tip (Section 21.6) becomes too large to be neglected relative to the specimen thickness, and the stress state at the tip deviates from the ideal plane strain (triaxial constraint) and approaches plane stress. In a state close to plane stress, plastic deformation occurs more easily, so the apparent fracture toughness is measured as a larger value than the true (plane-strain) $K_{\mathrm{IC}}$. Therefore, the value obtained in this test cannot be used in design as the $K_{\mathrm{IC}}$ intrinsic to the material, and it must be measured again with a thicker specimen.

Exercise 21.5: The shape of the R-curve and how fracture occurs

Solution

Under a constant stress $\sigma$, the energy release rate $\mathcal G(a)=\pi\sigma^2a/E'$ is a straight line through the origin, proportional to the crack half-length $a$ (or to the crack extension $\Delta a$). The theorem of Chapter 21 (the condition for stable and unstable crack growth) states that $\mathcal G=R$ is necessary for the crack to extend even slightly, and that, given this, the relative magnitudes of $\dd\mathcal G/\dd a$ and $\dd R/\dd a$ determine stability or instability.

For a material with a flat R-curve (constant, $\dd R/\dd a=0$) (an ideal brittle material such as glass), as the stress is raised little by little, the straight line of $\mathcal G$ reaches the constant value of $R$ at some stress. At that moment, $\dd\mathcal G/\dd a>0=\dd R/\dd a$ holds, and the instability condition is satisfied immediately. Therefore, the crack shows exactly the behavior of the Griffith theory that we saw in Section 21.2: “nothing happens until then, and the instant a certain stress is reached, it accelerates without warning and fractures.”

For a material with a rising R-curve, at the point where the line of $\mathcal G$ first reaches the curve (the intersection), the slope $\dd R/\dd a$ of the curve is still larger than the slope $\dd\mathcal G/\dd a$ of the line, so the stability condition ($\dd\mathcal G/\dd a\lt\dd R/\dd a$) is satisfied, and the crack stops there for the time being. When the stress is raised further, the slope of the line of $\mathcal G$ also increases (the slope is proportional to $\pi\sigma^2/E'$, so the larger $\sigma$ is, the steeper it becomes), and when the line finally comes to touch the R-curve, the growth becomes unstable beyond that point. In other words, in this material the crack fractures in stages: “each time the stress is raised, it extends stably by a little, and finally, at a certain stress, it goes all at once into unstable fracture.”

Practical advantage: In a material with a rising R-curve, the strength becomes less sensitive to how large the initial crack (flaw) was. Since even a small crack becomes unstable only after it has extended to some extent, the final fracture stress tends to be insensitive to the scatter in the initial crack size. This is an important property for suppressing the scatter in the strength of ceramics (related to the Weibull statistics that we will learn in Chapter 22), and it is one of the reasons why transformation toughening of zirconia and bridging toughening of silicon nitride (Section 21.7) are valued in practical materials.

Exercise 21.6: Situations in which small-scale yielding fails

Solution

As we saw in Chapter 21, the size of the plastic zone at the crack tip is $r_{\mathrm p}=\dfrac{1}{2\pi}(K_{\mathrm I}/\sigma_{\mathrm y})^2$ in Irwin's estimate and $r_{\mathrm D}=\dfrac\pi8(K_{\mathrm I}/\sigma_{\mathrm y})^2$ in the Dugdale model, and both are proportional to $(K_{\mathrm I}/\sigma_{\mathrm y})^2$. Therefore, the plastic zone becomes large (i) when $K_{\mathrm I}$ (that is, the stress or the crack size) is large, or (ii) when the yield stress $\sigma_{\mathrm y}$ is small (a soft material).

Specific example: Consider a member that has a relatively large crack (or notch) and is made of a material such as mild steel (low-carbon steel), whose yield stress is relatively low ($\sigma_{\mathrm y}\sim200$–$300\ \mathrm{MPa}$ or so) and which is highly ductile and can withstand a fairly large $K_{\mathrm I}$ without fracturing. In this case, the size of the plastic zone at the tip can reach the order of a few millimeters to, at times, several tens of millimeters, as calculated for structural steel in Example 21.6 of this chapter. If the plate thickness of the member, or the remaining dimension from the crack to the edge of the member (the ligament), is of the same order as the size of this plastic zone or smaller, the premise of small-scale yielding (that the plastic zone can be regarded as a “small point” relative to the whole member) breaks down. Specifically, this situation applies, for example, to a thin plate-like member (a plate thickness of a few millimeters) made of soft steel and having a relatively large notch or weld defect.

What happens if $K_{\mathrm{IC}}$ is used as it is: In a situation where small-scale yielding does not hold, the stress field around the crack tip no longer follows the elasticity solution given by the equation for the crack-tip K field. The material yields over a wide region around the tip and large-scale plastic deformation occurs (when the whole structure yields, it is called general yielding), so the state at the tip can no longer be represented by the single number $K_{\mathrm I}$. In such a case, if $K_{\mathrm{IC}}$, which is based only on elasticity theory, is used as the basis of the safety factor, the actual fracture behavior (behavior close to ductile fracture, in which the member ruptures after large plastic deformation) cannot be captured correctly, and there is a risk of an excessively conservative evaluation (or, conversely, a non-conservative evaluation that overlooks plastic collapse, which is a different failure mode). In such cases, it is necessary to use methods of elastic-plastic fracture mechanics, such as the J-integral introduced in Section 21.6.4 of this chapter.

Exercise 21.7: Identifying the mode from the direction of loading

Solution

Based on the definitions in Section 21.3.2, we identify the mode by focusing on the direction in which, and the manner in which, the crack surfaces are displaced.

(1) Under a tensile stress normal to the crack plane, the upper and lower faces of the crack are displaced in the direction in which they move apart from each other (the direction that opens the faces). This is a deformation in which the crack opens under tension normal to the crack plane, and it is Mode I (opening mode). The stress intensity factor $K_{\mathrm I}$ and the stress field that we have treated so far in this chapter all represent this situation.

(2) The upper and lower faces on either side of the crack are displaced in opposite directions, parallel to the direction of crack propagation—this is a shear deformation within the crack plane, and it is Mode II (in-plane shear mode). It differs from Mode I in that the crack faces hardly open at all and instead slide within the plane.

(3) In the torsion of a round bar, the faces are displaced out of plane in the direction parallel to the front of an axial crack (the direction of rotation about the crack front as the axis). This is an out-of-plane shear deformation in which the faces slide parallel to the crack front, and it is Mode III (out-of-plane shear mode). As stated in Section 21.3.2, torsional fracture of a round bar is a typical example in which Mode III appears.

In this way, (1)–(3) each involve a different direction of displacement, and Modes I, II, and III can be identified by distinguishing whether the crack faces “open perpendicularly,” “slide within the plane,” or “slide out of the plane.” Note also that in actual members multiple modes often coexist, but as stated in Section 21.3.2, the crack in a brittle material tends to advance in the direction perpendicular to the maximum principal stress, so even under oblique loading it tends to curve naturally toward the direction in which Mode I dominates.

← Back to Chapter 21, “Fundamentals of Fracture Mechanics — Stress Concentration, Griffith Theory, and Fracture Toughness”

Chapter 22 Fatigue, Scatter in Strength, and Environment-Assisted Fracture

Exercise 22.1: Infinite-life design and safe-life design

Solution

Consider a material whose S–N curve clearly shows an upper limit of stress amplitude (the fatigue limit $\sigma_{\mathrm w}$) below which failure never occurs however many times the loading is repeated (this is common in body-centered cubic metals such as steel). If the stress amplitude in service is always kept below $\sigma_{\mathrm w}$, then in theory fatigue failure will not occur no matter how many times the loading is repeated. This design policy is called infinite-life design. It is often adopted for components that continue to receive repeated loads of the same amplitude over long periods, such as automobile coil springs and bearings of stationary machines.

In contrast, for a material that shows no clear fatigue limit, such as an aluminum alloy (this is common in face-centered cubic metals), no matter how far the stress amplitude is lowered, failure will in theory eventually occur if the number of cycles is made large enough. For this reason, one first fixes a design life, that is, “how many cycles (or how many years, or tens of thousands of flight hours) the part is to be used safely,” then uses Basquin's law and the Paris law to calculate the allowable stress amplitude corresponding to that life, and keeps the actual working stress at or below it. This design policy is called safe-life design. Typical examples include the airframe of an aircraft (aluminum alloys are widely used there to save weight, and in addition the fatigue limit cannot be relied on); this approach is often combined with an operating practice (damage tolerance design) that assumes cracks are present, checks for their presence and size by periodic inspection, and replaces parts if necessary.

To sum up, infinite-life design is the philosophy of “keep the stress below the fatigue limit and do not worry about the life itself,” whereas safe-life design is the philosophy of “the fatigue limit cannot be used, so calculate the life and manage it.” We see that the design policy to be adopted changes fundamentally depending on whether the material has a fatigue limit.

Exercise 22.2: Magnitude of the Paris-law exponent $n$ and the behavior of crack growth

Solution

The expression for the fatigue life obtained in Chapter 22 by integrating the Paris law (for $n\neq2$) has the form

$$ N_{\mathrm f}=\frac{a_{\mathrm c}^{\,p}-a_0^{\,p}}{p\,C(Y\Delta\sigma)^{n}\pi^{n/2}},\qquad p=1-\frac n2 $$

Suppose $a_0\ll a_{\mathrm c}$ and $n\gt2$ ($p\lt0$). Then $a_{\mathrm c}^{\,p}$ is small compared with $a_0^{\,p}$ and can be neglected, so $N_{\mathrm f}\approx a_0^{\,p}\big/\{|p|\,C(Y\Delta\sigma)^n\pi^{n/2}\}$; that is, approximately $N_{\mathrm f}\propto(\Delta\sigma)^{-n}$ (because $(\Delta\sigma)^n$ appears in the denominator). (Strictly speaking $a_{\mathrm c}$ also changes with the stress, but the effect is small.) Therefore, when the stress range $\Delta\sigma$ is multiplied by $k$, the life becomes about $k^{-n}$ times its original value. Moreover, since the crack growth rate is $\dd a/\dd N=C(\Delta K)^n\propto(\Delta\sigma)^n$, for a crack of the same length the growth rate becomes $k^{n}$ times as large.

For a material with a large $n$ (for example, $n=10$ or so), raising the stress range by only 10% ($k=1.1$) sharply reduces the life to about $1.1^{-10}\approx0.386$ times its original value—less than half—and makes the growth rate about $1.1^{10}\approx2.6$ times as large. Conversely, lowering the stress only slightly extends the life greatly. In other words, a material with a large $n$ has a crack growth rate that is extremely sensitive to stress, so that a small error in estimating the design stress has a large effect on the life prediction; such a material is hard to handle (but, conversely, a long life is easy to obtain as long as the stress is kept sufficiently low).

On the other hand, for a material with a small $n$ ($n=2$–$3$ or so), the change in life for the same 10% change in stress is much more gradual (for $n=3$ the life is $1.1^{-3}\approx0.75$ times its original value and the growth rate is $1.1^3\approx1.33$ times). With such a material, even a modest error in the stress estimate does not throw the life prediction far off, which makes it easy for the designer to handle—but note that whether the absolute life at a given stress is long or short depends not only on $n$ but also on the coefficient $C$, so one cannot simply label a material “good” or “bad” by the size of $n$ alone.

Exercise 22.3: Limitations of Miner's rule

Solution

Miner's rule $D=\sum_i n_i/N_i$ assumes that, whatever the order in which the stress amplitudes are applied, the same combination of $(n_i,N_i)$ gives the same damage $D$. In real materials, however, when a large stress is applied first, local plastic deformation occurs around the crack tip, and after unloading a compressive residual stress may remain in that region. For the small stress amplitudes that follow, this residual stress acts in the direction that hinders the opening and closing of the crack and thus slows crack growth (the crack-growth retardation effect due to overloads). Conversely, if a compressive overload is applied first, the subsequent fatigue life may actually become shorter. Such an effect, which depends on “the order in which the loads were applied,” is called the load sequence effect.

Miner's rule is an approximation that takes no account whatsoever of such load sequence effects and simply adds the contributions of the individual amplitudes linearly. For this reason, for actual load patterns (in particular random loading, in which large and small loads are mixed irregularly), the prediction of Miner's rule can be either an overestimate or an underestimate of the actual life. In the design of important structures (the main structure of an aircraft, piping in a nuclear power plant, and so on), it is essential to use Miner's rule for an initial simple assessment while verifying the design by full-scale fatigue tests that reproduce load patterns close to the real ones, and by more detailed analyses that take load sequence effects into account (for example, a cumulative damage assessment combined with the rainflow counting method, which counts pairs of stress amplitude and number of cycles from an irregular time history of load).

Exercise 22.4: Magnitude of the Weibull modulus $m$ and the spread of the strength distribution

Solution

The equation of the straight line in the Weibull plot is

$$ \ln\ln\frac1{1-P_{\mathrm f}}=m\ln\sigma-m\ln\sigma_0 $$

Its slope is exactly the Weibull modulus $m$. For the line with $m=20$ (steep slope), a small change in the horizontal-axis variable $\ln\sigma$ produces a large change in the vertical axis (and hence in the failure probability $P_{\mathrm f}$)—in other words, the range of stress over which the failure probability goes from nearly 0% to nearly 100% is narrow. This means that the strength values are concentrated very close to the characteristic strength $\sigma_0$ (the scatter is small).

On the other hand, for the line with $m=5$ (gentle slope), the same change in the horizontal axis (stress) produces only a small change in the vertical axis (failure probability). This means that a wider range of stress is needed for the failure probability to go from nearly 0% to nearly 100%, which corresponds to the strength values being widely scattered around $\sigma_0$ (the scatter is large). In short, the steeper the slope of the Weibull plot, the smaller the scatter, and the gentler the slope, the larger the scatter.

A designer who uses a material with a small $m$ (large scatter) in an actual structure must take measures such as the following: (i) carry out strength tests on a statistically sufficient number of specimens to determine $\sigma_0,m$ accurately; (ii) use as the design basis not the average strength but a considerably lower stress corresponding to an acceptable failure probability (for example, 0.1% or 0.01%); and (iii) take into account the size effect and the effective volume seen in Section 22.7, so as to estimate correctly the strength at the actual part size. Moreover, since a small $m$ itself reflects “large scatter in the flaws,” improving the manufacturing process (homogenizing the raw powder, controlling the sintering conditions, and so on) to reduce the scatter of the flaws and thereby increase $m$ is also a fundamental countermeasure.

Exercise 22.5: Difference between flexural strength and tensile strength

Solution

In Chapter 22 we showed by integration that the effective volume in the three-point bending test is

$$ V_{\mathrm{eff}}=\frac{V}{2(m+1)^2} $$

(here $V$ is the total volume between the supports). For example, when $m=10$, $V_{\mathrm{eff}}/V=1/\{2\times11^2\}=1/242\approx0.4\%$, so the volume that is “effectively under high stress” in the flexural test is only a tiny fraction of the whole specimen.

However, the size-effect formula of Weibull statistics, $\sigma_1/\sigma_2=(V_2/V_1)^{1/m}$, expressed the fact that “the larger the volume, the higher the probability of containing a flaw of dangerous size, and the lower the strength.” The effective volume in the flexural test is much smaller than the volume when the same specimen is tested in uniform tension (the whole specimen), so the flexural test is, so to speak, the same as measuring the strength with an “effectively very small specimen.” Since the smaller the volume, the higher the measured strength, as the relation

$$ \frac{\sigma_{\text{three-point bending}}}{\sigma_{\text{tensile}}}=\left(\frac{V}{V_{\mathrm{eff}}}\right)^{1/m}=\bigl[2(m+1)^2\bigr]^{1/m} $$

shows, the strength measured by three-point bending is systematically higher than the uniform tensile strength of the same material (about 1.73 times for $m=10$). This is why the “flexural strength” listed in ceramics catalogs is higher than the strength obtained when a uniform tensile stress is actually applied to a part, and using the catalog value as it is for the design of a large part risks overestimating the strength—in design, the strength must be converted by taking into account the difference between the effective volumes of the specimen and of the part.

Exercise 22.6: Environments in which stress corrosion cracking is likely to occur

Solution

Static fatigue of glass is triggered by moisture (humidity) in the atmosphere. The network structure of Si, the main component of glass ($\mathrm{Si\!-\!O\!-\!Si}$ bonds), is hydrolyzed by reaction with water molecules at the stressed crack tip ($\mathrm{Si\!-\!O\!-\!Si}+\mathrm{H_2O}\to2(\mathrm{Si\!-\!OH})$), and the crack grows slowly as the bonds are broken. Therefore, the higher the humidity of the environment, the more readily static fatigue proceeds, and conversely it hardly proceeds at all in a dry environment (in vacuum or in a dry inert gas).

In contrast, delayed fracture of high-strength steel due to hydrogen embrittlement is triggered by hydrogen atoms penetrating into the steel. Hydrogen atoms generated by manufacturing processes such as pickling and electroplating, or by the corrosion reaction itself, penetrate and diffuse into the interstitial sites of the steel lattice, and gather and accumulate especially at places where stress is concentrated and the lattice is distorted, such as dislocations and crack tips.
