This locally lowers the toughness of the steel and causes fracture after a time delay under constant stress. Therefore, the more an environment exposes the steel to routes by which hydrogen can enter (acidic cleaning solutions, corrosive aqueous solutions, hydrogen gas atmospheres, and so on), the more readily delayed fracture occurs.

Although the environmental factors differ (moisture and humidity in the former case, hydrogen in the latter), both cases have this in common: they can be understood through the mechanism of subcritical crack growth, in which, under a constant sustained stress, chemical species in the environment reach the crack tip and make a crack that would otherwise not advance until the fracture toughness $K_{\mathrm{IC}}$ is reached grow slowly at a stress intensity factor much smaller than $K_{\mathrm{IC}}$. In both cases the growth rate can be approximated by an equation of the same form, $v=AK_{\mathrm I}^n$, and the mathematical treatment is also common to both: the coefficient $A$ depends strongly on the ambient conditions (humidity, hydrogen concentration, and so on).

Exercise 22.7: Failure probability and allowable stress from the Weibull distribution

Solution

(1) In the formula $P_{\mathrm f}=1-\exp\left[-\dfrac V{V_0}\left(\dfrac\sigma{\sigma_0}\right)^m\right]$ of Chapter 22, substitute $V=V_0$, $\sigma=200\ \mathrm{MPa}$, $\sigma_0=300\ \mathrm{MPa}$, and $m=10$. Since $(200/300)^{10}=(2/3)^{10}\approx0.01734$,

$$ P_{\mathrm f}=1-e^{-0.01734}\approx0.0172\quad(\text{about}\ 1.7\%) $$

(2) Setting $P_{\mathrm f}=0.01$ gives $\exp[-(\sigma/\sigma_0)^m]=0.99$. Taking the natural logarithm of both sides, $(\sigma/\sigma_0)^m=-\ln0.99=0.010050$. Therefore,

$$ \sigma=\sigma_0\bigl[-\ln(1-P_{\mathrm f})\bigr]^{1/m}=300\times(0.010050)^{1/10}\ \mathrm{MPa}\approx300\times0.6313\ \mathrm{MPa}\approx189\ \mathrm{MPa} $$

Even for a material with a characteristic strength of $300\ \mathrm{MPa}$, the stress must be held to about 63% of that value to make the failure probability 1%.

(3) To obtain the same failure probability for a part of volume $8V_0$, the exponent $\dfrac V{V_0}\left(\dfrac\sigma{\sigma_0}\right)^m$ must be kept at the same value, so the allowable stress is multiplied by $(V_0/V)^{1/m}=8^{-1/10}\approx0.8123$, giving

$$ \sigma\approx189.4\times0.8123\ \mathrm{MPa}\approx154\ \mathrm{MPa} $$

The larger the volume of a part, the lower the allowable stress (the size effect).

Exercise 22.8: Rapid cooling from hot water to cold water

Solution

The surface thermal stress is given by the formula $\sigma=-E\alpha\Delta T/(1-\nu)$ of Chapter 22. For $\Delta T=-80\ \mathrm K$:

Soda-lime glass ($E=70\ \mathrm{GPa}$, $\nu=0.22$, $\alpha=9.0\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{K^{-1}}$, $\sigma_{\mathrm f}=50\ \mathrm{MPa}$):

$$ \sigma=\frac{70\times10^9\times9.0\times10^{-6}\times80}{1-0.22}\ \mathrm{Pa}=\frac{5.04\times10^7}{0.78}\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx64.6\ \mathrm{MPa} $$

This exceeds the strength of $50\ \mathrm{MPa}$, so the glass breaks. The maximum temperature difference that avoids breakage is $\Delta T_{\mathrm c}=\sigma_{\mathrm f}(1-\nu)/(E\alpha)=3.9\times10^7/6.3\times10^5\ \mathrm K\approx61.9\ \mathrm K$, which is smaller than $80\ \mathrm K$.

Fused quartz ($E=73\ \mathrm{GPa}$, $\nu=0.17$, $\alpha=0.55\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{K^{-1}}$, $\sigma_{\mathrm f}=70\ \mathrm{MPa}$):

$$ \sigma=\frac{73\times10^9\times0.55\times10^{-6}\times80}{1-0.17}\ \mathrm{Pa}=\frac{3.212\times10^6}{0.83}\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx3.87\ \mathrm{MPa} $$

This is far smaller than the strength of $70\ \mathrm{MPa}$, so the glass does not break. With $\Delta T_{\mathrm c}\approx1447\ \mathrm K$, a temperature difference of $80\ \mathrm K$ is no problem at all.

An ordinary glass cup can crack when boiling water is suddenly poured into it, whereas fused quartz apparatus resists cracking even when moved suddenly from boiling water or a flame into cold water. The difference arises because the coefficients of linear thermal expansion $\alpha$ differ by more than an order of magnitude ($9.0\times10^{-6}$ and $0.55\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{K^{-1}}$). Note that here we have assumed the most severe case, in which the surface temperature drops instantaneously; the temperature difference at which cracking actually begins is larger than this (see the note on the Biot number in Chapter 22).

Exercise 22.9: Life under the Paris law with $n=2$

Solution

Since this is pulsating loading with $R=0$, $\sigma_{\max}=\Delta\sigma=100\ \mathrm{MPa}$. From the fracture criterion at the maximum stress, $K_{\mathrm{IC}}=Y\sigma_{\max}\sqrt{\pi a_{\mathrm c}}$, the critical crack length is

$$ a_{\mathrm c}=\frac1\pi\left(\frac{K_{\mathrm{IC}}}{Y\sigma_{\max}}\right)^2=\frac1\pi\left(\frac{60}{1\times100}\right)^2\ \mathrm m=\frac{0.36}\pi\ \mathrm m\approx0.1146\ \mathrm m=114.6\ \mathrm{mm} $$

For $n=2$, separation of variables in $\dd a/\dd N=C(Y\Delta\sigma)^2\pi a$ gives $\int a^{-1}\dd a=\ln a$, so

$$ N_{\mathrm f}=\frac{1}{C(Y\Delta\sigma)^2\pi}\ln\frac{a_{\mathrm c}}{a_0}=\frac{1}{1.0\times10^{-11}\times(100)^2\times\pi}\ln\frac{0.1146}{0.001} $$

The denominator is $1.0\times10^{-11}\times10^4\times\pi=3.1416\times10^{-7}$ and $\ln114.6=4.7414$, so

$$ N_{\mathrm f}=\frac{4.7414}{3.1416\times10^{-7}}\approx1.51\times10^{7}\ \text{cycles} $$

Unit check: the unit of $C(Y\Delta\sigma)^2\pi$ is $\mathrm{m/cycle}\cdot(\mathrm{MPa\sqrt m})^{-2}\cdot\mathrm{MPa}^2=1/\mathrm{cycle}$, so $N_{\mathrm f}$ is a number of cycles. Because only the logarithm of the ratio of crack lengths matters, even if the initial crack becomes ten times as long ($10\ \mathrm{mm}$), $\ln(11.46)=2.44$ and the life is only about halved. This differs from a case in which the crack length enters as a power, as in Example 22.3 with $n=3$, in the way the life depends on the size of the initial crack.

← Back to Chapter 22, “Fatigue, Scatter in Strength, and Environment-Assisted Fracture”

Chapter 23 Heat and Temperature — Gas Laws and Changes of State

Exercise 23.1: Calculating a quantity of heat

Solution

We use $Q=mc\Delta T$ from Formula 23.1 ($m$: mass, $c$: specific heat, $\Delta T$: temperature change). Substituting for the water $m=100\ \mathrm{g}$, specific heat $c=4.2\ \mathrm{J/(g\cdot K)}$, and $\Delta T=60-10=50\ \mathrm{K}$, we obtain

$$ Q=100\times4.2\times50=21000\ \mathrm{J}=2.1\times10^4\ \mathrm{J} $$

This is the quantity of heat. Converting to calories, since $1\ \mathrm{cal}=4.2\ \mathrm{J}$, we get $Q=21000/4.2=5000\ \mathrm{cal}=5.0\ \mathrm{kcal}$ (this is exactly the quantity of heat for $100\ \mathrm{g}\times50\ \mathrm{K}=5000\ \mathrm{g\cdot K}$, from the definition of specific heat, “$1\ \mathrm{cal}$ to raise $1\ \mathrm{g}$ of water by $1\ \mathrm{K}$,” and it can also be checked directly by mental arithmetic).

Exercise 23.2: Computing a volume with Boyle's law

Solution

Since the temperature is constant, Boyle's law $p_1V_1=p_2V_2$ can be used. Substituting $p_1=1.0\times10^5\ \mathrm{Pa}$, $V_1=2.0\ \mathrm{L}$, and $p_2=2.0\times10^5\ \mathrm{Pa}$ and solving for $V_2$, we obtain

$$ V_2=\frac{p_1V_1}{p_2}=\frac{1.0\times10^5\times2.0}{2.0\times10^5}=1.0\ \mathrm{L} $$

Since the pressure has exactly doubled, the volume has exactly halved—this is perfectly consistent with Figure 23.3 (each time the volume is halved, the pressure doubles).

Exercise 23.3: Heat absorbed in an isochoric process

Solution

In an isochoric process the piston does not move, so no work is done on the gas ($W=0$), and by the first law of thermodynamics $Q=\Delta U$. For a monatomic ideal gas $\Delta U=\dfrac32nR\Delta T$, so substituting $n=1.0\ \mathrm{mol}$, $R=8.31\ \mathrm{J/(mol\cdot K)}$, and $\Delta T=100\ \mathrm{K}$, we obtain

$$ Q=\Delta U=\frac32\times1.0\times8.31\times100=\frac32\times831=1246.5\approx1.2\times10^3\ \mathrm{J} $$

This is the quantity of heat absorbed.

Exercise 23.4: Why the molar heat capacity at constant pressure is larger than that at constant volume

Solution

We start from the first law of thermodynamics $\Delta U=Q-p\Delta V$ (solving the equation for $Q$ gives $Q=\Delta U+p\Delta V$). In an isochoric process the volume does not change ($\Delta V=0$), so all of the heat $Q$ added goes into the increase $\Delta U$ in internal energy: $Q_{\text{isochoric}}=\Delta U$. In an isobaric process, on the other hand, the gas does work $p\Delta V(>0)$ on its surroundings corresponding to the increase in its volume, so part of the added heat is “used up” in this work, and only the remainder goes into raising the internal energy: $Q_{\text{isobaric}}=\Delta U+p\Delta V$.

To achieve the same temperature rise $\Delta T$ (for an ideal gas $\Delta U$ is determined by the temperature change alone, so both processes require the same $\Delta U$), in the isobaric process we must add extra heat equal to the work $p\Delta V(>0)$. That is, $Q_{\text{isobaric}}\gt Q_{\text{isochoric}}$, and through the definition of the molar heat capacity $C_{\ast,\mathrm m}=Q_\ast/(n\Delta T)$ we obtain $C_{p,\mathrm m}\gt C_{V,\mathrm m}$. In fact, when we compute this for a monatomic ideal gas in Section 23.7, the difference is exactly equal to the gas constant $R$ ($C_{p,\mathrm m}-C_{V,\mathrm m}=R$).

Exercise 23.5: Comparing adiabatic compression and isothermal compression

Solution

After adiabatic compression, the kinetic energy (temperature) of the gas molecules is larger.

In isothermal compression, the energy corresponding to the work done on the gas from outside escapes as heat to the surroundings (the constant-temperature bath) each time ($Q=W'\lt0$; that is, the gas keeps releasing heat). Since the internal energy of an ideal gas is determined by the temperature alone, $\Delta U=0$, and the temperature does not change either. In adiabatic compression, on the other hand, there is no place at all for heat to escape ($Q=0$), so all of the work done on the gas from outside is converted into an increase in internal energy, $\Delta U=W(\gt0)$, and the speed at which the gas molecules rebound increases, so the temperature rises.

When the gas is compressed by the same amount of volume, in isothermal compression the temperature (and hence the rise in pressure) is held down, whereas in adiabatic compression the increase in internal energy due to the compression accumulates directly as a temperature rise, so the pressure rises more steeply. This is the physical reason why the adiabat has a steeper slope than the isotherm in Figure 23.13 (in terms of equations, the slope at the same point is $-p/V$ for the isotherm and $-\gamma p/V$ ($\gamma\gt1$) for the adiabat, so the adiabat is steeper, as shown in Table 23.1).

Exercise 23.6: Finding the coefficient of volume expansion and the fractional volume change from the coefficient of linear expansion

Solution

(1) Substituting $\alpha=1.2\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{K^{-1}}$ into Formula 23.3 ($\beta=3\alpha$, which holds for isotropic materials), we obtain

$$ \beta=3\times1.2\times10^{-5}=3.6\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{K^{-1}} $$

(2) From the equation for volume expansion $V=V_0(1+\beta t)$, the relative increase in volume is $\Delta V/V_0=\beta\Delta T$ ($V_0$ is, strictly speaking, the volume at $0\ {}^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$, but the difference is only about $\beta\times20\approx7\times10^{-4}$, so it does not affect an answer with two significant figures). Substituting $\Delta T=100\ \mathrm{K}$, we obtain

$$ \frac{\Delta V}{V_0}=3.6\times10^{-5}\times100=3.6\times10^{-3}=0.36\ \% $$

This is the fractional volume change.

Exercise 23.7: Comparing sign conventions for work

Solution

In the convention of this book, $W$ is the work “done on the gas by its surroundings” ($W\gt0$ when the gas is compressed), and $W'$ is the work “done by the gas on its surroundings” ($W'\gt0$ when the gas expands); the two are related by $W'=-W$. The first law of thermodynamics can be written $\Delta U=Q+W$ using $W$, and $\Delta U=Q-W'$ using $W'$. In high school physics as well, there are cases in which one learns $\Delta U=Q+W$ ($W$: work done on the gas) and cases in which one learns $Q=\Delta U+W'$ ($W'$: work done by the gas), and these correspond directly to the way this book writes them.

In contrast, engineering textbooks and the like often adopt the convention of using the same letter $W$ to mean “the work done by the gas” (corresponding to $W'$ in this book). In this convention, the first law of thermodynamics is written $\Delta U=Q-W$ ($W$: work done by the gas).

Relating the two conventions, we obtain the following:

  • $W$ in this book (work done on the gas) = $-W$ in the engineering convention (the work done by the gas, with its sign reversed)
  • $W'$ in this book (work done by the gas) = $W$ in the engineering convention (work done by the gas, unchanged)

That is, the equation $\Delta U=Q-W'$ in this book expresses exactly the same content as what the engineering convention calls “$\Delta U=Q-W$” ($W$: work done by the gas). Looking only at the letters, this book's $\Delta U=Q+W$ and the engineering $\Delta U=Q-W$ seem to have opposite signs, but this is merely the same physical law written with a different choice of the “direction” of the quantity called work (whether the work done on the gas or the work done by the gas is taken as positive), so there is no contradiction. When you read other textbooks, it is a good idea to get into the habit of first checking which convention the book adopts.

← Back to Chapter 23, “Heat and Temperature — Gas Laws and Changes of State”

Chapter 24 The Kinetic Theory of Gases and the van der Waals Equation of State

Exercise 24.1: Mass of one Ar molecule

Solution

The molecular weight of Ar, $39.9$, is a dimensionless relative value, and it means that the molar mass (the mass per $1\ \mathrm{mol}$) is $M=39.9\ \mathrm{g/mol}$. Since $1\ \mathrm{mol}=N_{\mathrm A}$ molecules have a total mass of $M$ grams, the mass $m$ of one molecule is found by dividing the molar mass by the Avogadro constant $N_{\mathrm A}$:

$$ m=\frac{M}{N_{\mathrm A}}=\frac{39.9\ \mathrm{g/mol}}{6.022\times10^{23}\ \mathrm{mol^{-1}}}=6.63\times10^{-23}\ \mathrm g=6.63\times10^{-26}\ \mathrm{kg} $$

This is the result (we rounded $39.9/6.022=6.626$ to three significant figures). The order of magnitude of the $m$ obtained in this way (about $10^{-26}\ \mathrm{kg}$) is the same as that of the value used in Example 24.3 of Chapter 24 ($m=5.0\times10^{-26}\ \mathrm{kg}$), and we can confirm that it is a reasonable value.

Exercise 24.2: Ratio of the root-mean-square speeds of He gas and Ar gas

Solution

Solving the result of the kinetic theory of gases (Formula 24.1 of Chapter 24), $\frac12m\overline{|\bm v|^2}=\frac32\dfrac{R}{N_{\mathrm A}}T$, for $\overline{|\bm v|^2}$ gives $\overline{|\bm v|^2}=\dfrac{3RT}{mN_{\mathrm A}}=\dfrac{3RT}{M}$ ($M=mN_{\mathrm A}$ is the molar mass). At the same temperature $T$, the factor $3RT$ on the right-hand side is a common value independent of the kind of gas, so there is the proportionality $\overline{|\bm v|^2}\propto1/M$. Therefore the ratio of the root-mean-square speeds $\sqrt{\overline{|\bm v|^2}}$ is

$$ \frac{\sqrt{\overline{|\bm v|^2}}_{\mathrm{He}}}{\sqrt{\overline{|\bm v|^2}}_{\mathrm{Ar}}} =\sqrt{\frac{\overline{|\bm v|^2}_{\mathrm{He}}}{\overline{|\bm v|^2}_{\mathrm{Ar}}}} =\sqrt{\frac{3RT/M_{\mathrm{He}}}{3RT/M_{\mathrm{Ar}}}} =\sqrt{\frac{M_{\mathrm{Ar}}}{M_{\mathrm{He}}}} $$

Substituting the molecular weights (the numerical values of the molar masses) $4.00$ and $39.9$, we obtain

$$ \sqrt{\frac{M_{\mathrm{Ar}}}{M_{\mathrm{He}}}}=\sqrt{\frac{39.9}{4.00}}=\sqrt{9.975}\approx3.158 $$

That is, even at the same temperature, the molecules of the lighter He gas fly around about 3.16 times faster than those of Ar gas. This corresponds to the intuition that the lighter a molecule is, the faster it must move in order to keep the same kinetic energy (the same temperature).

Exercise 24.3: Physical meaning of the van der Waals constants $a,b$

Solution

The constant $b$ is a quantity that corrects for the fact that gas molecules are not point masses but have a finite size. The equation of state of an ideal gas assumed that the volume $V$ can be brought arbitrarily close to 0, but in reality the molecules cannot penetrate into one another any further, so no matter how large the pressure is made, the volume cannot become smaller than the volume that the molecules exclude ($b$ per $1\ \mathrm{mol}$). We therefore replace $V$ in the ideal gas equation by the effective volume $V-b$ in which the molecules can move freely. Note that $b$ is not simply $N_{\mathrm A}$ times the volume of the molecules themselves; it is the excluded volume resulting from the fact that the centers of molecules, regarded as spheres of radius $r$, cannot approach each other closer than $2r$, and it corresponds to $b\approx4N_{\mathrm A}\cdot\frac{4\pi}{3}r^3$ (about four times $N_{\mathrm A}$ times the volume of a molecule itself).

The constant $a$ is a quantity that corrects for the weak attractive force (intermolecular force, van der Waals force) acting between gas molecules. A molecule near the wall of the container receives, on average, an inward attractive force from the many molecules inside the container, so the force of its impact when it collides with the wall is slightly weakened, and the actual pressure is lower than the pressure predicted by the ideal gas model. This decrease is the product of the number of molecules striking the wall (proportional to the density $1/V$) and the attractive force that each molecule receives (also proportional to the density), so it is expected to be proportional to the square of the density, $1/V^2$. We therefore use, as a correction term $a/V^2$, the pressure $p+a/V^2$ that the ideal gas model predicts (with no intermolecular force) in place of the measured pressure $p$ ($V$ is the volume per $1\ \mathrm{mol}$).

To summarize, $b$ is the correction to the volume due to “molecular size” and $a$ is the correction to the pressure due to “attraction between molecules”; both are positive constants that express realistic deviations from the ideal gas (see Figure 24.7 of Chapter 24).

Exercise 24.4: Why does a gas not liquefy above the critical temperature?

Solution

Key point: For $T\gt T_{\mathrm c}$ the isotherm has no point where $\dd p/\dd V=0$; compressing the gas only increases its density continuously, and no boundary separating gas from liquid (a jump in density) appears. Therefore “liquefaction” (the change from gas to liquid) does not occur.

How to read Figure 24.5: An isotherm at low temperature has a trough and a peak, and a real gas behaves by replacing this interval with a horizontal region (the liquid–gas coexistence region) in which only the volume decreases greatly while the pressure hardly changes. The length of this region (its extent in the volume direction) gradually becomes shorter as the temperature is raised, and at the critical temperature (about $31\ °\mathrm C\approx304\ \mathrm K$ for $\mathrm{CO_2}$; for the van der Waals equation in Figure 24.5 as well, $T_{\mathrm c}\approx304\ \mathrm K$) it finally shrinks to a single point (the critical point C).

Check using the equation: The critical point is a degenerate inflection point at which both the first and second derivatives of the graph of $p=f(V)$ vanish (Definition 24.3 of Chapter 24). For the van der Waals equation, $\dfrac{\dd p}{\dd V}=-\dfrac{RT}{(V-b)^2}+\dfrac{2a}{V^3}$, so the condition for $\dfrac{\dd p}{\dd V}\lt0$ (a monotonic decrease at every volume) is

$$ RT\gt g(V)\equiv\frac{2a(V-b)^2}{V^3}\qquad(\text{for all }V\gt b\text{}) $$

Differentiating $g(V)$ gives $g'(V)=\dfrac{2a(V-b)(3b-V)}{V^4}$, and it takes its maximum value $g(3b)=\dfrac{2a\cdot(2b)^2}{27b^3}=\dfrac{8a}{27b}$ at $V=3b$. Therefore, “$\dd p/\dd V\lt0$ for all $V$” is equivalent to $RT\gt\dfrac{8a}{27b}$, that is, $T\gt\dfrac{8a}{27Rb}=T_{\mathrm c}$. For $T\gt T_{\mathrm c}$ the curve is a smooth monotonically decreasing curve with no trough, no peak, and no critical point, and only when the temperature is exactly $T_{\mathrm c}$ does a single point appear at which $\dd p/\dd V=0$, namely at $V=3b=V_{\mathrm c}$.

That the liquid–gas coexistence region (the horizontal part) does not exist at all means that the phenomenon itself, “as the pressure is raised, the gas suddenly changes into liquid at a certain volume,” does not occur. Therefore, for $T\gt T_{\mathrm c}$, no matter how much the pressure of the gas is raised, its volume merely shrinks smoothly and its density rises continuously, without passing through a state of coexistence with the liquid (that is, without producing a clear boundary at which one can say “from here on it is liquid”), and “liquefaction” in the ordinary sense does not occur.

Exercise 24.5: Re-deriving $C_{V,\mathrm m}=\frac32R$ from $U=\frac32nRT$

Solution

From Formula 24.2 of Chapter 24 (the internal energy of a monatomic ideal gas, which is obtained from both the kinetic theory of gases and the law of equipartition of energy (Law 24.2)), we have

$$ U=\frac32nRT $$

The molar heat capacity at constant volume is defined as the internal energy per $1\ \mathrm{mol}$ differentiated with respect to temperature (at constant volume):

$$ C_{V,\mathrm m}\equiv\frac1n\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial T}\right)_V $$

Here $\left(\dfrac{\partial U}{\partial T}\right)_V$ is a partial derivative, meaning that $U$ is differentiated with respect to $T$ alone, regarding $V$ as a constant. Differentiating $U=\frac32nRT$ with respect to $T$ (since $n,R$ are constants, this is the derivative of a linear function of $T$), we obtain

$$ \left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial T}\right)_V=\frac32nR $$

Therefore we obtain

$$ C_{V,\mathrm m}=\frac1n\times\frac32nR=\frac32R $$

Numerically, $C_{V,\mathrm m}=\frac32\times8.31\approx12.47\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)}$. This value agrees with the molar heat capacity at constant volume of a monatomic ideal gas obtained in Chapter 27 from the first law of thermodynamics by using the fact that $Q=\Delta U$ in an isochoric process (Chapter 27).

Exercise 24.6: Convergence to the ideal gas equation when $V$ is very large

Solution

Solving the van der Waals equation of state $\left(p+\dfrac{a}{V^2}\right)(V-b)=RT$ ($V$ is the volume per $1\ \mathrm{mol}$) for $p$ gives

$$ p=\frac{RT}{V-b}-\frac{a}{V^2} $$

For $V\gg b$, we rewrite $\dfrac1{V-b}=\dfrac1V\cdot\dfrac1{1-b/V}$ and, using the fact that $b/V$ is small, apply the binomial expansion (up to the first-order term) $\dfrac1{1-x}\approx1+x$ ($x=b/V$, $|x|\ll1$), which gives

$$ \frac{1}{V-b}=\frac1V\left(1+\frac bV+\cdots\right)\approx\frac1V+\frac{b}{V^2} $$

Substituting this into the expression for $p$, we obtain

$$ p\approx RT\left(\frac1V+\frac{b}{V^2}\right)-\frac{a}{V^2}=\frac{RT}{V}+\frac{RTb-a}{V^2} $$

Multiplying both sides by $V$, we obtain

$$ pV\approx RT+\frac{RTb-a}{V} $$

The first term $RT$ is the equation of state of an ideal gas itself, and the second term $\dfrac{RTb-a}{V}$ is a correction term that becomes smaller the larger $V$ is (it approaches 0 in proportion to $1/V$). Therefore, the fact that the second term vanishes and $pV\to RT$ as $V\to\infty$ agrees with the result confirmed in Example 24.11, but here we have further shown concretely that the size of the correction for finite $V$ is of order $1/V$. Note also that the sign of this correction term $RTb-a$ (which of $RTb$ and $a$ is larger) determines whether the pressure of a real gas is higher or lower than that of an ideal gas at the same temperature and volume—at high temperature and small $a$ (weak intermolecular force), the volume correction $b$ dominates and $p$ is higher than for an ideal gas, whereas at low temperature and large $a$ (strong intermolecular force), the pressure correction $a$ dominates and $p$ is lower than for an ideal gas.

Exercise 24.7: Change in the internal energy of diatomic molecules

Solution

From Formula 24.3 of Chapter 24, the internal energy of an ideal gas consisting of molecules with $f$ degrees of freedom is $U=\frac f2nRT$. When the temperature changes by $\Delta T$, the change in internal energy is (since $n,f,R$ are constant)

$$ \Delta U=\frac f2nR\,\Delta T $$

For $\mathrm{O_2}$, $f=5$ (three translational plus two rotational), $n=1\ \mathrm{mol}$, and $\Delta T=350-300=50\ \mathrm K$, so

$$ \Delta U_{\mathrm{O_2}}=\frac52\times1\times8.31\times50=1.04\times10^{3}\ \mathrm J\approx1.04\ \mathrm{kJ} $$

For Ar, $f=3$, so

$$ \Delta U_{\mathrm{Ar}}=\frac32\times1\times8.31\times50=6.23\times10^{2}\ \mathrm J\approx0.623\ \mathrm{kJ} $$

The ratio is $\Delta U_{\mathrm{O_2}}/\Delta U_{\mathrm{Ar}}=5/3\approx1.67$; to warm the gas by the same amount, $\mathrm{O_2}$, which has more degrees of freedom, requires about 1.7 times as much energy. This is because part of the added energy is also distributed to rotational motion.

← Back to Chapter 24, “The Kinetic Theory of Gases and the van der Waals Equation of State”

Chapter 25 Real Gases — The Principle of Corresponding States, the Compression Factor, and the Virial Expansion

Exercise 25.1: Reduced variables of oxygen

Solution

Substitute the van der Waals constants of oxygen, $a=0.138\ \mathrm{Pa\,m^6/mol^2}$ and $b=3.19\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{m^3/mol}$, into the formulas for the critical constants

$$ V_{\mathrm c}=3b,\qquad T_{\mathrm c}=\frac{8a}{27Rb},\qquad p_{\mathrm c}=\frac{a}{27b^2} $$

(Section 25.1.1). Taking $R=8.314\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)}$, we obtain

$$ T_{\mathrm c}=\frac{8\times0.138}{27\times8.314\times3.19\times10^{-5}}\ \mathrm K\approx154.2\ \mathrm K $$ $$ p_{\mathrm c}=\frac{0.138}{27\times(3.19\times10^{-5})^2}\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx5.02\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx49.6\ \mathrm{atm} $$

(The measured values are $T_{\mathrm c}\approx154.6\ \mathrm K$ and $p_{\mathrm c}\approx49.8\ \mathrm{atm}$, but since $a,b$ are values determined so as to fit the critical constants, it is natural that the calculated values nearly agree with them. The small differences come from rounding $a,b$ to three significant figures.) Next, substituting $T=250\ \mathrm K$ and $p=20\ \mathrm{atm}$ into the definitions of the reduced variables, $T_{\mathrm r}=T/T_{\mathrm c},\ p_{\mathrm r}=p/p_{\mathrm c}$, gives

$$ T_{\mathrm r}=\frac{250}{154.2}\approx1.62,\qquad p_{\mathrm r}=\frac{20}{49.6}\approx0.40 $$

The value $T_{\mathrm r}\approx1.62$ is higher than the critical temperature, but it is not as far from the critical point as nitrogen is in Example 25.2 ($T_{\mathrm r}\approx2.38$).

Exercise 25.2: The physical meaning of the principle of corresponding states

Solution

What the principle of corresponding states (Law 25.1) asserts is that the van der Waals equation of state written in reduced variables

$$ \left(p_{\mathrm r}+\frac{3}{V_{\mathrm r}^2}\right)(3V_{\mathrm r}-1)=8T_{\mathrm r} $$

is just a single equation that does not depend at all on $a,b$ (and hence on the kind of gas). Therefore, if gases A and B are at the same $T_{\mathrm r},p_{\mathrm r}$, the same numbers are substituted into this single equation (a cubic equation), so the resulting real solution for the reduced volume $V_{\mathrm r}$ is also exactly the same for A and B. That is,

$$ V_{\mathrm r}(\mathrm A)=V_{\mathrm r}(\mathrm B) $$

This equality holds (here, if the equation has more than one real solution, we compare the same branch—the gas branch if the state is a gas, the liquid branch if it is a liquid).

Next, for the compression factor, we rewrite the definition $Z=pV_{\mathrm m}/(RT)$ in terms of the reduced variables by substituting $p=p_{\mathrm r}p_{\mathrm c}$, $V_{\mathrm m}=V_{\mathrm r}V_{\mathrm c}=3bV_{\mathrm r}$, and $T=T_{\mathrm r}T_{\mathrm c}$:

$$ Z=\frac{p_{\mathrm r}p_{\mathrm c}\cdot3bV_{\mathrm r}}{R\,T_{\mathrm r}T_{\mathrm c}}=\frac{3p_{\mathrm c}b}{RT_{\mathrm c}}\cdot\frac{p_{\mathrm r}V_{\mathrm r}}{T_{\mathrm r}} $$

Substituting here $p_{\mathrm c}=a/(27b^2)$ and $T_{\mathrm c}=8a/(27Rb)$, we find that $\dfrac{3p_{\mathrm c}b}{RT_{\mathrm c}}=\dfrac{3\times a/(27b^2)\times b}{R\times8a/(27Rb)}=\dfrac{a/(9b)}{8a/(27b)}=\dfrac{27}{9\times8}=\dfrac38$, which is again a universal constant independent of $a,b$. Therefore,

$$ Z=\frac38\cdot\frac{p_{\mathrm r}V_{\mathrm r}}{T_{\mathrm r}} $$

Hence, if gases A and B are at the same $(p_{\mathrm r},T_{\mathrm r})$ (and therefore at the same $V_{\mathrm r}$), then $Z(\mathrm A)=Z(\mathrm B)$ also holds. To summarize, under the principle of corresponding states, different gases in the same reduced state have the same reduced volume and the same compression factor—this is the meaning of “corresponding.” Note that $Z_{\mathrm c}=3/8$ is the value for a van der Waals gas; for real gases the critical compression factor is different, about $0.27$–$0.29$, so the principle of corresponding states holds only approximately for real gases.

Exercise 25.3: The Boyle temperature and the ideality of real gases

Solution

The virial equation of state (Definition 25.3) is

$$ Z=1+\frac{B(T)}{V_{\mathrm m}}+\frac{C}{V_{\mathrm m}^2}+\cdots $$

By definition (Definition 25.4), the Boyle temperature $T_{\mathrm B}$ is the temperature at which this second virial coefficient vanishes, $B(T_{\mathrm B})=0$. So when we substitute $T=T_{\mathrm B}$, the first term that shifts $Z$ away from $1$ (the term proportional to $1/V_{\mathrm m}$, which has the largest effect) vanishes exactly, and only

$$ Z=1+\frac{C}{V_{\mathrm m}^2}+\cdots $$

remains. The $C/V_{\mathrm m}^2$ term is proportional to $1/V_{\mathrm m}^2$, a quantity that tends to zero much faster (at second order) than the $1/V_{\mathrm m}$ term, so even if the molar volume $V_{\mathrm m}$ is not very large (even if the pressure is not very low), the approximation $Z\approx1$ holds over a fairly wide range. On the other hand, at temperatures $T\neq T_{\mathrm B}$ the term proportional to $1/V_{\mathrm m}$ remains, so as $V_{\mathrm m}$ is increased (the pressure is lowered), we approach $Z=1$ only slowly, at $1$st order. Therefore, the Boyle temperature is the special temperature at which a real gas behaves like an ideal gas over the widest range of pressures.

Exercise 25.4: The compression factor and the size of the volume

Solution

Solving the definition of the compression factor, $Z=pV_{\mathrm m}/(RT)$, for $V_{\mathrm m}$ gives

$$ V_{\mathrm m}=\frac{ZRT}{p}=Z\times\frac{RT}{p}=Z\,V_{\mathrm m}^{\mathrm{ideal}} $$

(here $V_{\mathrm m}^{\mathrm{ideal}}=RT/p$ is the molar volume of an ideal gas at the same $p,T$). At the same $p,T$, $V_{\mathrm m}^{\mathrm{ideal}}$ is a positive constant, so $V_{\mathrm m}$ is proportional to $Z$. Therefore,

  • When $Z\lt1$: $V_{\mathrm m}=Z\,V_{\mathrm m}^{\mathrm{ideal}}\lt V_{\mathrm m}^{\mathrm{ideal}}$; that is, the actual molar volume is smaller than that of the ideal gas.
  • When $Z\gt1$: $V_{\mathrm m}=Z\,V_{\mathrm m}^{\mathrm{ideal}}\gt V_{\mathrm m}^{\mathrm{ideal}}$; that is, the actual molar volume is larger than that of the ideal gas.

This agrees with the interpretation in Section 25.2 ($Z\lt1$ means that attraction dominates and the molecules are “drawn together,” so the volume becomes smaller; $Z\gt1$ means that repulsion due to the size of the molecules themselves dominates, so the volume becomes larger).

Exercise 25.5: Conditions under which the higher-order terms of the virial equation of state can be neglected

Solution

In the virial equation of state $Z=1+B/V_{\mathrm m}+C/V_{\mathrm m}^2+\cdots$, the ratio of the third term to the second term is

$$ \frac{C/V_{\mathrm m}^2}{B/V_{\mathrm m}}=\frac{C}{B\,V_{\mathrm m}} $$

Since $B,C$ are constants determined by the gas and its temperature (in order of magnitude, they are the first and second powers, respectively, of a volume of about the size of $b$), this ratio is sufficiently small—that is, the terms from $C/V_{\mathrm m}^2$ onward may be neglected and the first-order approximation $Z\approx1+B/V_{\mathrm m}$ suffices—when

$$ V_{\mathrm m}\gg\left|\frac{C}{B}\right| $$

that is, when the molar volume $V_{\mathrm m}$ is sufficiently large (the gas is sufficiently dilute). In terms of pressure, since $V_{\mathrm m}\approx RT/p$ (an estimate valid when the gas is close to ideal), a large $V_{\mathrm m}$ corresponds to a sufficiently low pressure $p$. To summarize, the higher-order terms of the virial expansion can be neglected in the limit of low pressure and large volume (a dilute gas), and the more we raise the pressure and compress the gas (the smaller the molar volume becomes), the more we must take into account the higher-order terms involving $C,D,\ldots$.

Let us find a concrete rule of thumb for nitrogen. For a van der Waals gas, $B=b-a/(RT)$ and $C=b^2$, so $C/B=b^2/\bigl(b-a/(RT)\bigr)$. At $T=300\ \mathrm K$, $B=3.87\times10^{-5}-0.137/(8.314\times300)\approx-1.62\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{m^3/mol}$ and $C=(3.87\times10^{-5})^2\approx1.50\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm{m^6/mol^2}$, so

$$ \left|\frac{C}{B}\right|\approx\frac{1.50\times10^{-9}}{1.62\times10^{-5}}\approx9.2\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{m^3/mol} $$

The condition for the third term to be at most $10\ \%$ of the second term, $\left|C/(BV_{\mathrm m})\right|\le0.1$, is $V_{\mathrm m}\ge9.2\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{m^3/mol}$. Converting this to a pressure with the ideal-gas value $V_{\mathrm m}\approx RT/p$ gives

$$ p\lesssim\frac{RT}{V_{\mathrm m}}=\frac{8.314\times300}{9.2\times10^{-4}}\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx2.7\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx27\ \mathrm{atm} $$

That is, for nitrogen at $300\ \mathrm K$, the first-order approximation $Z\approx1+B/V_{\mathrm m}$ suffices at pressures below about $27\ \mathrm{atm}$, and at higher pressures the $C$ term can no longer be neglected.

Exercise 25.6: The distance at which the sign of the intermolecular potential changes

Solution

Setting $U(r)=4\varepsilon\bigl[(\sigma/r)^{12}-(\sigma/r)^6\bigr]=0$ gives

$$ \left(\frac{\sigma}{r}\right)^{12}=\left(\frac{\sigma}{r}\right)^{6} $$

Let $X\equiv(\sigma/r)^6$. The left-hand side can be written as $(\sigma/r)^{12}=X^2$, so the equation becomes

$$ X^2=X\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad X^2-X=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad X(X-1)=0 $$

so $X=0$ or $X=1$. The root $X=(\sigma/r)^6=0$ corresponds to $r\to\infty$ (it merely expresses the fact that the potential approaches zero asymptotically far away; it is not a finite distance at which the sign changes), so the solution we want is $X=1$. From $(\sigma/r)^6=1$ we get $\sigma/r=1$, that is,

$$ r=\sigma $$

For $r\lt\sigma$ we have $\sigma/r\gt1$, so $(\sigma/r)^{12}\gt(\sigma/r)^6$ and $U\gt0$ (the potential energy is positive: the molecules are too close together and the energy is high), whereas for $r\gt\sigma$ the opposite holds, $U\lt0$ (the energy is negative, a region of attractive binding)—this is precisely the boundary between the blue and red regions in Figure 25.6.

Next, we examine the direction of the force at this $r=\sigma$. The force is $F=-\dd U/\dd r=\dfrac{24\varepsilon\sigma^6}{r^{7}}\left(\dfrac{2\sigma^6}{r^6}-1\right)$, and at $r=\sigma$ it is $F=\dfrac{24\varepsilon}{\sigma}(2-1)=\dfrac{24\varepsilon}{\sigma}\gt0$, that is, a repulsive force. The force becomes $0$ at $r=2^{1/6}\sigma\approx1.12\,\sigma$ (the distance at which $U$ is minimal), and that is where the force changes from repulsive to attractive. Thus the distance $r=\sigma$ at which the sign of $U$ changes and the distance $r_{\min}=2^{1/6}\sigma$ at which the direction of the force changes are different.

Exercise 25.7: The Boyle temperatures of hydrogen and carbon dioxide

Solution

(1) We substitute the van der Waals constants into $T_{\mathrm B}=a/(Rb)$ and $T_{\mathrm c}=8T_{\mathrm B}/27$. For hydrogen,

$$ T_{\mathrm B}=\frac{0.0248}{8.314\times2.66\times10^{-5}}\ \mathrm K\approx112\ \mathrm K,\qquad T_{\mathrm c}=\frac{8}{27}\times112\ \mathrm K\approx33.2\ \mathrm K $$

and for carbon dioxide,

$$ T_{\mathrm B}=\frac{0.364}{8.314\times4.27\times10^{-5}}\ \mathrm K\approx1025\ \mathrm K,\qquad T_{\mathrm c}=\frac{8}{27}\times1025\ \mathrm K\approx304\ \mathrm K $$

(The values of $T_{\mathrm c}$ agree well with the measured values, $304\ \mathrm K$ for carbon dioxide and $33.2\ \mathrm K$ for hydrogen.)

(2) For hydrogen, $273\ \mathrm K$ is higher than $T_{\mathrm B}\approx112\ \mathrm K$, so $B\gt0$; concretely,

$$ B=b-\frac{a}{RT}=2.66\times10^{-5}-\frac{0.0248}{8.314\times273}\approx2.66\times10^{-5}-1.09\times10^{-5}\approx+1.57\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{m^3/mol} $$

For carbon dioxide, $273\ \mathrm K$ is far lower than $T_{\mathrm B}\approx1025\ \mathrm K$, so $B\lt0$, and

$$ B=4.27\times10^{-5}-\frac{0.364}{8.314\times273}\approx4.27\times10^{-5}-16.04\times10^{-5}\approx-1.18\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{m^3/mol} $$

For $p=10\ \mathrm{atm}=1.01325\times10^6\ \mathrm{Pa}$, $V_{\mathrm m}\approx RT/p=8.314\times273/(1.01325\times10^6)\approx2.24\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{m^3/mol}$, so

$$ Z_{\mathrm{H_2}}\approx1+\frac{1.57\times10^{-5}}{2.24\times10^{-3}}\approx1.007,\qquad Z_{\mathrm{CO_2}}\approx1+\frac{-1.18\times10^{-4}}{2.24\times10^{-3}}\approx0.947 $$

(Solving the van der Waals equation directly gives $1.007$ and $0.945$, so the first-order approximation is in good agreement.)

(3) In Figure 25.5, the $Z$ of hydrogen rises gently above $Z=1$ ($Z\gt1$) because $273\ \mathrm K$ is higher than the $T_{\mathrm B}$ of hydrogen and $B\gt0$ (the effect of molecular size dominates). For carbon dioxide, $273\ \mathrm K$ is far lower than $T_{\mathrm B}$, so $B\lt0$ (attraction dominates) and $Z\lt1$. Because how high $T_{\mathrm B}$ is (the size of $a/b$) differs from gas to gas, the direction in which $Z$ changes can be opposite even at the same temperature.

Exercise 25.8: The stability of the S-shaped isotherm

Solution

(1) The reduced equation of state at $T_{\mathrm r}=0.9$ is $p_{\mathrm r}=8T_{\mathrm r}/(3V_{\mathrm r}-1)-3/V_{\mathrm r}^2$, and its slope is $\dd p_{\mathrm r}/\dd V_{\mathrm r}=-24T_{\mathrm r}/(3V_{\mathrm r}-1)^2+6/V_{\mathrm r}^3$. At $V_{\mathrm r}=1.2$, $3V_{\mathrm r}-1=2.6$, so

$$ p_{\mathrm r}=\frac{7.2}{2.6}-\frac{3}{1.44}\approx2.769-2.083=0.686,\qquad \frac{\dd p_{\mathrm r}}{\dd V_{\mathrm r}}=-\frac{21.6}{6.76}+\frac{6}{1.728}\approx-3.195+3.472=+0.277 $$

At $V_{\mathrm r}=2.0$, $3V_{\mathrm r}-1=5$, so

$$ p_{\mathrm r}=\frac{7.2}{5}-\frac{3}{4}=1.44-0.75=0.690,\qquad \frac{\dd p_{\mathrm r}}{\dd V_{\mathrm r}}=-\frac{21.6}{25}+\frac{6}{8}=-0.864+0.75=-0.114 $$

(2) The state $V_{\mathrm r}=1.2$, where the slope $\dd p_{\mathrm r}/\dd V_{\mathrm r}\gt0$, is one in which the pressure rises when the volume is increased, and it is mechanically unstable. This is also consistent with its lying between the spinodal points $0.719$ and $1.529$ of Example 25.4. On the other hand, $V_{\mathrm r}=2.0$, where the slope is negative, is mechanically stable. However, since $1.529\lt2.0\lt2.349=V_{\mathrm r}^{\mathrm{gas}}$, it lies on the side smaller than the volume $2.349$ of the coexisting gas (the higher-pressure side at the same temperature), so it is not a thermodynamic equilibrium state but is metastable (supersaturated vapor). Only the part with $V_{\mathrm r}\gt2.349$ is a stable gas.

Chapter 26 Thermodynamic Systems and State Quantities — Quasi-Static Processes and Work

Exercise 26.1: Everyday examples of path functions

Solution

As an everyday example of path functions, take the distance a car travels and the fuel it consumes. Consider going from point A to point B either by a straight road ($30\ \mathrm{km}$) or by a roundabout road over a mountain ($50\ \mathrm{km}$). The correspondence is as follows.

Thermodynamic termThis example
Initial state and final stateStarting point A and destination B
State function (such as $U$)Position (elevation). At the moment of arriving at B, the value is the same whichever road was taken
Path (the way the process is carried out)Which road was taken, and how it was driven
Path function (such as $Q$, $W$)Distance traveled and amount of fuel consumed. They differ depending on the road: $30\ \mathrm{km}$ or $50\ \mathrm{km}$

Even when the starting point and the end point (the result) are the same, the distance traveled and the fuel consumed differ if the route (the history) differs. Similarly, even to bring about the same change in internal energy $\Delta U$, the values of the heat $Q$ and the work $W'$ done by the system differ if the path differs (Example 26.7).

(Alternative solution: the electricity bill. Even to keep the same room at the same temperature (the initial and final states are the same), the amount of electric energy consumed changes depending on whether the air conditioner is switched on and off frequently or left running. The state corresponds to the room temperature, the path corresponds to the way the air conditioner is operated, and the path function corresponds to the electricity bill.)

Exercise 26.2: Why the degrees of freedom of a diatomic molecule change with temperature

Solution

The motion of a diatomic molecule can be divided into three kinds: (a) translational motion of the molecule as a whole (three directions), (b) rotational motion about the two axes perpendicular to the molecular axis, and (c) vibrational motion in which the distance between the two atoms periodically stretches and shrinks. When the energy can be written in terms of quadratic terms (terms in the square of a velocity or a coordinate), each such term counts as one degree of freedom. Translation gives three terms such as $\tfrac12mv_x^2$; rotation gives one term $\tfrac12I\omega^2$ per axis, hence two terms; and vibration gives two terms, the kinetic energy $\tfrac12\mu\dot r^2$ and the elastic energy $\tfrac12k(r-r_0)^2$.

Near room temperature, the typical size of the energy exchanged in collisions between molecules (of the order of the thermal energy $k_{\mathrm B}T$) is much smaller than the energy needed to excite vibration (the energy spacing of the vibrational quantum, determined by the stiffness of the spring of the interatomic bond). Consequently, near room temperature the vibrational motion is hardly excited at all; it is effectively in a “freeze-out” state and takes no part in the exchange of energy. Therefore, near room temperature only 3 translational + 2 rotational = 5 degrees of freedom are effectively used. The temperature that serves as a guide to whether vibration matters (the characteristic temperature) is about $3400\ \mathrm K$ for $\mathrm{N_2}$ and about $2300\ \mathrm K$ for $\mathrm{O_2}$, and room temperature, $300\ \mathrm K$, is far below these.

When the temperature becomes sufficiently high, the collision energy between molecules becomes comparable to the energy needed to excite vibration, and the vibrational motion can also take part in the exchange of energy (the vibration “thaws”). Since vibration has two independent quadratic terms, the kinetic energy and the elastic energy, the degrees of freedom increase by two. As a result, at high temperatures the degrees of freedom become $5+2=7$. (A detailed explanation of the fact that vibration is a quantum-mechanical phenomenon that requires a “quantum” for excitation is something that can be understood after studying quantum mechanics, and goes beyond the scope of this book. Here it is enough to grasp the qualitative picture that “near room temperature vibration is effectively not used, and begins to be used only at high temperatures.”)

Exercise 26.3: What does it mean to say that a quasi-static process is “ideal but unrealistic”?

Solution

A quasi-static process is, by definition, a process carried out “infinitely slowly.” However, any real operation must be completed in a finite time—expanding a gas over literally infinite time is impossible in practice. In this sense, the quasi-static process is merely an “unrealistic” idealization (one that cannot be realized strictly in reality). That said, “slowly” here means slowly compared with the time it takes for disturbances inside the gas to even out (about $3\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm s$ for sound to travel $10\ \mathrm{cm}$), so an operation such as moving a piston at $1\ \mathrm{cm/s}$ can be regarded as nearly quasi-static.

Nevertheless, the quasi-static process is regarded as “ideal” because, thanks to this idealization, the gas can be regarded as being in thermal equilibrium at every instant during the change (macroscopically uniform, with the pressure and temperature fixed at definite values everywhere). Only an instant at which the gas is in thermal equilibrium can be represented unambiguously as a single point on the $p$-$V$ diagram (Section 26.4). In a quasi-static process, all intermediate states from the starting point to the end point are states of thermal equilibrium, so the whole process can be drawn as a continuous curve on the $p$-$V$ diagram; moreover, the external pressure and the gas pressure balance ($p_{\mathrm{ex}}=p$), so integral calculations such as the work $W'=\int p\,\dd V$ are also well defined. In contrast, in the middle of an abrupt change (an irreversible process), convection and turbulence arise in the gas and destroy the thermal equilibrium, so that “intermediate state” cannot be represented as a single point in the first place—because the pressure and volume take different values at different places.

In other words, the phrase “ideal but unrealistic” means “although it is a limiting idealization that cannot be realized strictly, it is precisely thanks to this idealization that the whole process of change can be treated mathematically in a well-defined way.” All real changes are irreversible to a greater or lesser degree, and the quasi-static process is used as a standard (a yardstick) against which to compare them.

Exercise 26.4: Why a polyatomic molecule has six degrees of freedom

Solution

A bent triatomic molecule (for example $\mathrm{H_2O}$) has its three atoms not lying on one straight line (it is not a linear molecule), so there are three independent directions of axes about which the molecule can rotate while keeping its overall shape (rotations about three mutually perpendicular axes of rotation). Because none of the moments of inertia about the three axes is $0$, three terms of the rotational kinetic energy $\tfrac12I\omega^2$ appear. Therefore, the rotational degrees of freedom number three, and together with the three translational degrees of freedom we obtain $f=3+3=6$ (vibration excluded).

In contrast, a diatomic molecule has a straight line connecting its two atoms (the molecular axis), and rotating the molecule about this molecular axis does not change the shape of the molecule (the arrangement of the atoms) at all—since the two atoms can be regarded as point-like particles (point masses), the moment of inertia about the molecular axis is $0$, and rotation about this axis carries no energy (it is indistinguishable from “nothing happening” even mechanically). For this reason, rotation about the molecular axis is not counted as a degree of freedom. What remains is only rotation about the two directions perpendicular to the molecular axis, and this is why the rotational degrees of freedom of a diatomic molecule are two (not three). A bent molecule has no such “axis about which rotation is indistinguishable,” so rotations in all three directions count as meaningful degrees of freedom, and the rotational degrees of freedom are three.

Linear polyatomic molecules such as $\mathrm{CO_2}$ and $\mathrm{C_2H_2}$, whose atoms lie on a straight line, have a molecular axis just as diatomic molecules do, and the moment of inertia about that axis is $0$, so the only rotations are about the two axes perpendicular to the molecular axis. Therefore, the degrees of freedom of a linear molecule, excluding vibration, are 3 translational + 2 rotational, that is, $f=5$, and not $f=6$.

Exercise 26.5: The reason for the sign in the general expression for work

Solution

The sign of the general expression for work $\dd'W=-p_{\mathrm{ex}}\dd V$ ($W$ is the “work done on the system”) reflects the physical fact that when a gas expands, the gas is the side that gives energy to the surroundings, not the side that receives it.

When the gas expands ($\dd V\gt0$), the gas pushes the piston outward and does work on the surroundings (the other side of the piston)—that is, the gas is giving energy to the surroundings. Since it is giving energy, the work “done on” the gas cannot be positive; it must instead be negative. Indeed, substituting $\dd V\gt0$ into $\dd'W=-p_{\mathrm{ex}}\dd V$ gives $\dd'W\lt0$ (since $p_{\mathrm{ex}}\gt0$), so the signs correspond correctly.

Conversely, when the gas is compressed ($\dd V\lt0$), the surroundings are pouring in energy to compress the gas, so positive work must be being done on the gas; substituting $\dd V\lt0$ into $\dd'W=-p_{\mathrm{ex}}\dd V$ gives $\dd'W\gt0$, and here too the signs correspond correctly.

For the work done by the system, $W'=-W$, the sign is exactly opposite: during expansion ($\dd V\gt0$), $\dd'W'=p_{\mathrm{ex}}\dd V\gt0$ (the gas does positive work on the surroundings), and during compression ($\dd V\lt0$), $\dd'W'\lt0$ (far from doing work on the surroundings, the gas is simply having work done on it). In this way, the minus sign in $\dd'W=-p_{\mathrm{ex}}\dd V$ is the sign needed to reflect correctly, in the quantity $W$ (the work done on the system), the physical direction: “during expansion the gas is the side that gives out energy, and during compression the gas is the side that receives energy.”

In the language of mechanics, the external force (the force due to the external pressure) pushes the gas, together with the piston, inward, and during expansion, in which the piston moves outward, the direction of the force and the direction of the displacement are opposite, so the work done by the external force on the gas is negative. This is the origin of the sign in $\dd'W=(-F)\,\dd x=-p_{\mathrm{ex}}S\,\dd x=-p_{\mathrm{ex}}\dd V$.

Exercise 26.6: The difference between exact and inexact differentials

Solution

Integrating the infinitesimal change in internal energy $\dd U$ from state $i$ to state $f$ gives, as in Eq. (26.5) of Chapter 26,

$$ \Delta U=\int_i^f\dd U=U_f-U_i $$

so only the two endpoints of the interval of integration (the initial state $i$ and the final state $f$) need to be specified, and the answer ($U_f-U_i$) is determined uniquely, no matter how the path in between is specified. This is the meaning of saying that $\dd U$ is an exact differential—under the integral sign only “from $i$ to $f$” is written, and no information about the path is needed.

On the other hand, when the infinitesimal heat $\dd'Q$ is integrated, as in Eq. (26.6) of Chapter 26,

$$ Q=\int_{i,\,\text{path}}^{f}\dd'Q $$

the information “path” must be specified explicitly under the integral sign. Even between the same initial state $i$ and final state $f$, if the route followed differs, the result of the integral (the amount of heat exchanged) can differ. This is the meaning of saying that $\dd'Q$ is an inexact differential—to determine the result of the integral uniquely, one must specify not only the endpoints but also the path itself in between.

To summarize, the difference is that for an exact differential “the value of the integral is determined by the endpoints alone” (that is, the original quantity is a state function), whereas for an inexact differential “the value of the integral is not determined unless the path in between is also specified” (that is, the original quantity is a path function). The infinitesimal quantity of work $\dd'W$ is also an inexact differential, like heat, so it is written with a prime.

Exercise 26.7: Change in the internal energy of a monatomic ideal gas (calculation problem)

Solution

By the formula of this chapter, the internal energy of a monatomic ideal gas (with $f=3$ degrees of freedom) is $U=\dfrac32nRT$. When the volume is held constant and only the temperature is changed by $\Delta T$, the change in internal energy is obtained by fixing $n$ and taking out only the change in $T$, so we have

$$ \Delta U=\frac{3}{2}nR\,\Delta T $$

Substituting $n=3\ \mathrm{mol}$, $R=8.3145\ \mathrm{J/(mol\cdot K)}$, $\Delta T=80\ \mathrm K$, we obtain

$$ \Delta U=\frac{3}{2}\times3\ \mathrm{mol}\times8.3145\ \mathrm{J/(mol\cdot K)}\times80\ \mathrm K $$

Computing the coefficient first, $\dfrac32\times3=4.5$, so we obtain

$$ \Delta U=4.5\times8.3145\times80\ \mathrm J=4.5\times665.16\ \mathrm J\approx2993\ \mathrm J\approx2.99\ \mathrm{kJ} $$

Checking the units, $[\mathrm{mol}]\times[\mathrm{J/(mol\cdot K)}]\times[\mathrm K]=[\mathrm J]$, which agrees with the unit of internal energy (the joule).

Exercise 26.8: Heat and work along a straight-line path

Solution

State $\mathrm A$ is ($1.0\times10^5\ \mathrm{Pa}$, $2.0\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{m^3}$) and state $\mathrm B$ is ($2.0\times10^5\ \mathrm{Pa}$, $4.0\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{m^3}$). As found in Example 26.7, since the internal energy is a state function, the change in internal energy is independent of the path: $\Delta U=9.0\times10^3\ \mathrm J$.

Path c is the straight line joining $\mathrm A$ and $\mathrm B$ on the $p$-$V$ diagram. Since the process is quasi-static, $W'$ equals the area under this line (Formula 26.4, $W'=\int p\,\dd V$). The figure under the line is a trapezoid with upper base $p_{\mathrm A}=1.0\times10^5\ \mathrm{Pa}$, lower base $p_{\mathrm B}=2.0\times10^5\ \mathrm{Pa}$, and height $V_{\mathrm B}-V_{\mathrm A}=2.0\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{m^3}$, so

$$ W'_{\mathrm c}=\frac{p_{\mathrm A}+p_{\mathrm B}}{2}\,(V_{\mathrm B}-V_{\mathrm A})=\frac{1.0\times10^5+2.0\times10^5}{2}\ \mathrm{Pa}\times2.0\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{m^3}=3.0\times10^3\ \mathrm J $$

(the same value is obtained by integrating the straight line $p=p_{\mathrm A}+\dfrac{p_{\mathrm B}-p_{\mathrm A}}{V_{\mathrm B}-V_{\mathrm A}}(V-V_{\mathrm A})$ from $V_{\mathrm A}$ to $V_{\mathrm B}$). From the first law $\Delta U=Q-W'$, we obtain

$$ Q_{\mathrm c}=\Delta U+W'_{\mathrm c}=9.0\times10^3+3.0\times10^3=1.2\times10^4\ \mathrm J $$

Compare this with path a ($W'=4.0\times10^3\ \mathrm J$, $Q=1.3\times10^4\ \mathrm J$) and path b ($W'=2.0\times10^3\ \mathrm J$, $Q=1.1\times10^4\ \mathrm J$): the $W'$ and $Q$ of path c are exactly intermediate between them. Even if the initial and final states are the same, $W'$ and $Q$ differ depending on the path, and only $\Delta U$ is independent of the path.

Exercise 26.9: Converting between work done by and work done on the system

Solution

(a) Since the system released heat, $Q=-300\ \mathrm J$; since work was done on it by the surroundings, the work done on the system is $W=+200\ \mathrm J$, and the work done by the system is $W'=-W=-200\ \mathrm J$.

In the formula $\Delta U=Q+W$, which uses the work done on the system,

$$ \Delta U=Q+W=-300+200=-100\ \mathrm J $$

In the formula $\Delta U=Q-W'$, which uses the work done by the system,

$$ \Delta U=Q-W'=-300-(-200)=-100\ \mathrm J $$

Both give $\Delta U=-100\ \mathrm J$, in agreement. The gas received $200\ \mathrm J$ through work but lost $300\ \mathrm J$ as heat, so its internal energy decreases by $100\ \mathrm J$.

(b) $Q=+120\ \mathrm J$, and since the system did $50\ \mathrm J$ of work on the surroundings, the work done by the system is $W'=50\ \mathrm J$. In the formula $\Delta U=Q-W$ of the convention that writes the “work done by the system” as $W$, the $W$ corresponds to $W'$ in this book, so the value to substitute is $50\ \mathrm J$ (a positive value). Then we have

$$ \Delta U=Q-W=120-50=70\ \mathrm J $$

Calculating in the notation of this book as $\Delta U=Q-W'=120-50=70\ \mathrm J$, or using the work done on the system, $W=-50\ \mathrm J$, as $\Delta U=Q+W=120+(-50)=70\ \mathrm J$, gives the same result.

Exercise 26.10: Confirming that $\dd'W$ and $\dd'Q$ are inexact differentials

Solution

The criterion for $M\,\dd x+N\,\dd y$ to be an exact differential form is $\partial M/\partial y=\partial N/\partial x$.

(a) Writing $\dd'W=-p\,\dd V=0\cdot\dd p+(-p)\,\dd V$, the coefficient of $\dd p$ is $M=0$ and the coefficient of $\dd V$ is $N=-p$.

$$ \pdiff{M}{V}=\pdiff{0}{V}=0,\qquad\pdiff{N}{p}=\pdiff{(-p)}{p}=-1 $$

The two partial derivatives differ, since $0\ne-1$, so the criterion does not hold. Therefore $\dd'W$ is not an exact differential form (it is an inexact differential).

(b) For a quasi-static change of a monatomic ideal gas, $p=nRT/V$, so

$$ \dd'Q=\frac32nR\,\dd T+p\,\dd V=\frac32nR\,\dd T+\frac{nRT}{V}\,\dd V $$

Regarding $(T,V)$ as the variables, let the coefficient of $\dd T$ be $M=\tfrac32nR$ and the coefficient of $\dd V$ be $N=\dfrac{nRT}{V}$. Then

$$ \pdiff{M}{V}=0,\qquad\pdiff{N}{T}=\frac{nR}{V} $$

The two partial derivatives differ, since $0\ne nR/V$, so the criterion does not hold. Therefore $\dd'Q$ is not an exact differential form (it is an inexact differential).

(c) Dividing $\dd'Q$ by $T$ gives

$$ \frac{\dd'Q}{T}=\frac{3nR}{2T}\,\dd T+\frac{nR}{V}\,\dd V $$

Let $M=\dfrac{3nR}{2T}$ and $N=\dfrac{nR}{V}$. Then

$$ \pdiff{M}{V}=\pdiff{}{V}\left(\frac{3nR}{2T}\right)=0,\qquad\pdiff{N}{T}=\pdiff{}{T}\left(\frac{nR}{V}\right)=0 $$

The two coincide, so the criterion holds. Indeed, we can write $\dfrac{\dd'Q}{T}=\dd\left(\dfrac32nR\ln T+nR\ln V\right)$. The heat $Q$ itself is not a function of state, but dividing by $T$ turns it into the infinitesimal change of a function of state (an exact differential). This $1/T$ is the integrating factor, and it is the starting point of the entropy that we study in Chapter 29.

← Back to Chapter 26, “Thermodynamic Systems and State Quantities — Quasi-Static Processes and Work”

Chapter 27 Heat Capacity, Enthalpy, and the Joule–Thomson Effect

Exercise 27.1: The difference between the heat capacities at constant volume and at constant pressure

Solution

The first law (with this book's sign convention, in which the work is that done on the gas) is $\dd U=\dd'Q+\dd'W$, $\dd'W=-p\,\dd V$.

Isochoric process: Since $\dd V=0$, $\dd'W=-p\,\dd V=0$. Therefore $\dd U=\dd'Q$; that is, the heat received goes entirely into increasing the internal energy. Because the volume does not change, the gas does no work of expansion at all.

Isobaric process: As the temperature rises, the gas also expands (for an ideal gas, $pV=nRT$ shows that if $T$ rises at constant $p$, then $V$ increases in proportion). In this case the gas does work $\dd'W'=-\dd'W=p\,\dd V\gt0$ on its surroundings. The heat received, $\dd'Q=\dd U+p\,\dd V$ (the first law rewritten), must supply both the increase $\dd U$ in internal energy and this work of expansion $p\,\dd V$.

When the gas receives the same quantity of heat $\dd'Q$, the increase in internal energy is $\dd U=\dd'Q$ at constant volume and $\dd U=\dd'Q-p\,\dd V\lt\dd'Q$ at constant pressure. At constant pressure it is smaller by the work of expansion $p\,\dd V$. For an ideal gas $\dd U=C_V\,\dd T$, so to obtain the same $\dd T$, the constant-pressure process needs $p\,\dd V$ more heat.

Comparing the heat needed to raise the temperature by the same $\dd T$, the constant-pressure process requires extra heat equal to “the amount spent on the work of expansion.” This is the reason why $C_p=(\partial H/\partial T)_p$ is larger than $C_V=(\partial U/\partial T)_V$ (in general $C_p\ge C_V$; Chapter 27, Formula 27.3). As we saw in Example 27.2 of Chapter 27, for an ideal gas this extra heat is exactly $nR\,\dd T$ (which is precisely Mayer's relation $C_p-C_V=nR$).

Exercise 27.2: Equivalence of the two forms of Poisson's formula

Solution

One form of Poisson's formula in Chapter 27 is $T_1V_1^{\gamma-1}=T_2V_2^{\gamma-1}$. By the ideal gas law $pV=nRT$ we have $T=pV/(nR)$, so we substitute this into both sides:

$$ \frac{p_1V_1}{nR}\,V_1^{\gamma-1}=\frac{p_2V_2}{nR}\,V_2^{\gamma-1} $$

Canceling the constant $nR$ (which is not $0$) common to both sides, we get

$$ p_1V_1\cdot V_1^{\gamma-1}=p_2V_2\cdot V_2^{\gamma-1} $$

On the left side, $V_1\cdot V_1^{\gamma-1}=V_1^{1+(\gamma-1)}=V_1^\gamma$ (the law of exponents $x^a\cdot x^b=x^{a+b}$), and likewise the right side becomes $V_2^\gamma$, so that

$$ p_1V_1^{\gamma}=p_2V_2^{\gamma} $$

is obtained. This is exactly the other form of the formula. Therefore, as long as we use the ideal gas law in the form $T=pV/(nR)$, the two forms are completely equivalent, and we may start from either one.

Exercise 27.3: Why the internal pressure of an ideal gas is zero

Solution

The internal pressure $\pi_T=(\partial U/\partial V)_T$ is the quantity that expresses how much the internal energy changes when only the average spacing between molecules (the volume) is changed without changing the temperature.

In an ideal gas, we assume that the molecules exert no forces on one another at all (one of the basic assumptions of the kinetic theory of gases). In that case, the internal energy of the gas consists solely of the sum of the kinetic energies of the thermal motion of all the molecules. Even if we change the average distance between molecules (the volume), no forces (attractive or repulsive) act between the molecules in the first place, so there is no potential energy that could be stored there from the start. The sum of the kinetic energies is determined only by the temperature (a quantity related to the square of the average speed of the molecules) and does not depend on how far apart the molecules are. Therefore, even if we change $V$ (the volume, and hence the intermolecular distance) while keeping $T$ constant, $U$ does not change at all, and $\pi_T=(\partial U/\partial V)_T=0$.

In a real gas in which attractive forces act between the molecules (for example, the van der Waals gas of Figure 27.5 in Chapter 27), the situation is different. When the molecules attract one another, to increase the volume and pull the molecules apart we must do work against that attraction—this is exactly the same reasoning as the potential energy increasing when we lift an object against the Earth's gravity. To that extent, the potential energy due to the intermolecular forces is added to the internal energy, and $U$ increases as the volume is made larger. Therefore $\pi_T=(\partial U/\partial V)_T\gt0$ (in Figure 27.5 of Chapter 27, we confirmed, with the concrete form $U(V)=-an^2/V$, that $U$ increases monotonically as $V$ is made larger).

Exercise 27.4: Why the Joule–Thomson coefficient is defined for an isenthalpic process

Solution

If we replace the Joule–Thomson process (the process of forcing a gas through a porous barrier inside an adiabatic container) by two pistons, then since the whole container is thermally insulated (no heat flows in or out), the net work received by the gas becomes directly the change in internal energy. Combining the work $+p_1V_1$ done by the left piston in pushing the gas in and the work $-p_2V_2$ done in pushing back the right piston, we have $\Delta U=p_1V_1-p_2V_2$, and rearranging this gives $U_1+p_1V_1=U_2+p_2V_2$, that is, $H_1=H_2$ (the enthalpy does not change) (see the derivation in Chapter 27).

In other words, the path of change that actually occurs when “a gas is forced through a porous barrier” is, mathematically, nothing other than a path along which “the pressure $p$ and the temperature $T$ change while the enthalpy $H$ is held constant.” When we want to express how one physical quantity (here the temperature $T$) changes in response to a change in another physical quantity (the pressure $p$), it is natural to take the partial derivative with the quantity actually held constant along that path written as a subscript (the same idea as in $C_V=(\partial U/\partial T)_V$ being a partial derivative along the “path of constant volume”). In the Joule–Thomson process, the “quantity held constant along the path” was the enthalpy, so defining $\mu=(\partial T/\partial p)_H$ as a partial derivative at constant enthalpy is the definition that correctly represents the phenomenon that actually occurs.

Exercise 27.5: Why producing liquid helium requires three stages of cooling

Solution

For a gas to be cooled by Joule–Thomson expansion ($\mu\gt0$), the state of the gas, in temperature and pressure, must lie inside the inversion curve in Figure 27.10 of Chapter 27. If it is not inside the inversion curve (that is, it is outside the curve, or in the low-pressure region where the curve does not extend at all), the gas will instead heat up on expansion ($\mu\lt0$).

Looking at Figure 27.10, the sizes of the inversion curves of the three gases are quite different—the inversion curve of nitrogen extends the farthest, that of hydrogen is much smaller, and that of helium is smaller still (the curves become smaller in the order N$_2$, H$_2$, He). The maximum inversion temperature (the high-temperature value as $p\to0$) is, according to measurements, approximately $620\ \mathrm{K}$ for N$_2$, $200\ \mathrm{K}$ for H$_2$, and $40\ \mathrm{K}$ for He (Figure 27.10 is a rough guide based on the van der Waals model, and its values differ, but the trend of decreasing in this order is the same). Room temperature (approximately $300\ \mathrm{K}$) lies inside the inversion curve of nitrogen, but not inside the inversion curves of hydrogen or helium at all.

Therefore, even if we expand helium at room temperature, it is not inside the small curve of helium in the first place (the inside of that curve being, at best, temperatures of about $40\ \mathrm{K}$ or lower), so it is not cooled by the Joule–Thomson effect. So first we make liquid nitrogen ($77\ \mathrm{K}$) by Joule–Thomson expansion of nitrogen at room temperature (since it is inside nitrogen's own wide inversion curve). Next, so that the temperature is below the maximum inversion temperature of hydrogen (approximately $200\ \mathrm{K}$), we precool hydrogen to $77\ \mathrm{K}$ with liquid nitrogen and then let it undergo Joule–Thomson expansion, producing liquid hydrogen ($20\ \mathrm{K}$). Finally, so that the temperature is below the maximum inversion temperature of helium (approximately $40\ \mathrm{K}$), we precool helium to $20\ \mathrm{K}$ with liquid hydrogen and then let it undergo Joule–Thomson expansion, and only then do we obtain liquid helium. The very structure in which the maximum inversion temperature of the gas at each stage decreases in the order N$_2$, H$_2$, He is the reason why helium cannot be liquefied in a single stage and must be precooled in sequence, in a cascade.

Exercise 27.6: Comparing the work done by a gas in adiabatic and isothermal expansion

Solution

(1) For reversible isothermal expansion, by the equation of Chapter 27 ($W'=nRT\ln(V_2/V_1)$, the same form as in Example 27.1),

$$ W'_{\text{isothermal}}=nRT_1\ln\frac{V_2}{V_1}=1.0\times8.314\times300\times\ln\frac{20}{10} $$

Since $\ln2\approx0.6931$,

$$ W'_{\text{isothermal}}=8.314\times300\times0.6931\approx1728.8\ \mathrm{J}\approx1.73\ \mathrm{kJ} $$

(2) For reversible adiabatic expansion, we first find the temperature after expansion using Poisson's formula $T_1V_1^{\gamma-1}=T_2V_2^{\gamma-1}$ of Chapter 27:

$$ T_2=T_1\left(\frac{V_1}{V_2}\right)^{\gamma-1}=300\times\left(\frac{10}{20}\right)^{0.4}=300\times0.5^{0.4} $$

Since $0.5^{0.4}=e^{0.4\ln0.5}=e^{0.4\times(-0.6931)}=e^{-0.2773}\approx0.7579$,

$$ T_2\approx300\times0.7579\approx227.4\ \mathrm{K} $$

Since the process is adiabatic, $Q=0$, and therefore (with this book's sign convention) $\Delta U=W$. The work done by the gas on its surroundings is $W'=-W=-\Delta U=-C_V(T_2-T_1)=C_V(T_1-T_2)$. Since this is an ideal gas of diatomic molecules, $C_V=\tfrac52 nR=2.5\times1.0\times8.314=20.785\ \mathrm{J/K}$. Hence,

$$ W'_{\text{adiabatic}}=C_V(T_1-T_2)=20.785\times(300-227.4)=20.785\times72.6\approx1509.9\ \mathrm{J}\approx1.51\ \mathrm{kJ} $$

(3) $W'_{\text{isothermal}}\approx1.73\ \mathrm{kJ}$ is larger than $W'_{\text{adiabatic}}\approx1.51\ \mathrm{kJ}$. As we confirmed in Figure 27.3 of Chapter 27, when we compare the isotherm and the adiabat passing through the same starting point $(V_1,p_1)$, on the side where $V$ increases (the direction of expansion) the adiabat is steeper ($\abs{\dd p/\dd V}=\gamma p/V\gt p/V$) and passes below the isotherm. That is, in isothermal expansion the gas expands while always maintaining a larger pressure for the same change in volume, so the area under the curve in the $p$-$V$ diagram (= the work) is larger. (On the side where $V$ decreases, that is, in the direction of compression, the adiabat is conversely the upper one.) Physically, this corresponds to the following difference: in adiabatic expansion the gas sacrifices its own internal energy (its temperature falls) by the amount of work it does, so the pressure drops more, whereas in isothermal expansion heat is supplied from outside to keep the temperature constant (and correspondingly more work can be extracted). (For the numerical values, see the check below.)

Check: With $n=1.0,\ R=8.314,\ T_1=300,\ V_1=10,\ V_2=20,\ \gamma=1.4$, we have $W'_{\text{isothermal}}=nRT_1\ln(V_2/V_1)\approx1728.85\ \mathrm{J}$, $T_2=T_1(V_1/V_2)^{\gamma-1}\approx227.36\ \mathrm{K}$, and $W'_{\text{adiabatic}}=C_V(T_1-T_2)\approx1509.87\ \mathrm{J}$. Since $1728.85\gt1509.87$, this agrees with the conclusion of (3).

Exercise 27.7: $C_p-C_V$ of copper

Solution

(1) The bulk modulus $K$ and the isothermal compressibility $\kappa_T$ are reciprocals of each other, $K=1/\kappa_T$, so

$$ \kappa_T=\frac{1}{K}=\frac{1}{1.4\times10^{11}\ \mathrm{Pa}}\approx7.1\times10^{-12}\ \mathrm{Pa^{-1}} $$

(2) We use the general Mayer's relation (Formula 27.3 of Chapter 27) per $1\ \mathrm{mol}$:

$$ C_{p,\mathrm m}-C_{V,\mathrm m}=\frac{\alpha^2TV_{\mathrm m}}{\kappa_T}=\alpha^2TV_{\mathrm m}K $$

Multiplying $\alpha^2=(4.9\times10^{-5})^2=2.401\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm{K^{-2}}$ by $T=300\ \mathrm{K}$ gives $7.203\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{K^{-1}}$; multiplying by $V_{\mathrm m}=7.1\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{m^3/mol}$ gives $5.114\times10^{-12}\ \mathrm{m^3/(mol\,K)}$; and multiplying by $K=1.4\times10^{11}\ \mathrm{Pa}$ gives

$$ C_{p,\mathrm m}-C_{V,\mathrm m}\approx0.72\ \mathrm{Pa\,m^3/(mol\,K)}=0.72\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)} $$

(3) Relative to $C_{p,\mathrm m}\approx24.5\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)}$, the ratio is $0.72/24.5\approx0.03$, that is, about $3\,\%$. A solid hardly expands when heated ($\alpha$ is small) and, moreover, is hard to compress ($K$ is large), so even when it is heated at constant pressure, little of the heat goes into the work of expansion. For this reason we may regard $C_p\approx C_V$. This is in contrast to an ideal gas, for which the difference is as large as $C_p-C_V=R\approx8.3\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)}$.

Exercise 27.8: Throttling expansion of nitrogen and hydrogen

Solution

In the Joule–Thomson process, $\dd H=0$. Setting $\dd H=0$ in the total differential of the enthalpy, $\dd H=-\mu C_p\,\dd p+C_p\,\dd T$, gives $\dd T=\mu\,\dd p$. Regarding $\mu$ as constant and integrating, we get $\Delta T=\mu\,\Delta p$. The change in pressure is $\Delta p=1.0-10.0=-9.0\ \mathrm{atm}$.

(1) Nitrogen: $\Delta T=0.25\ \mathrm{K/atm}\times(-9.0\ \mathrm{atm})=-2.25\ \mathrm{K}\approx-2.3\ \mathrm{K}$. The temperature falls by about $2.3\ \mathrm{K}$, becoming $297.75\ \mathrm{K}$.

(2) Hydrogen: $\Delta T=(-0.03\ \mathrm{K/atm})\times(-9.0\ \mathrm{atm})=+0.27\ \mathrm{K}$. The temperature rises by about $0.3\ \mathrm{K}$, becoming $300.27\ \mathrm{K}$.

(3) The maximum inversion temperature of nitrogen (approximately $620\ \mathrm{K}$ by measurement) is higher than room temperature, $300\ \mathrm{K}$, so room temperature lies inside the inversion curve and $\mu\gt0$ (cooling). The maximum inversion temperature of hydrogen (approximately $200\ \mathrm{K}$) is lower than room temperature, and room temperature lies outside the inversion curve, so $\mu\lt0$ (heating). Thus, for the same change in pressure, nitrogen is cooled while hydrogen is warmed slightly. To cool hydrogen by throttling expansion, it must be precooled in advance to below its maximum inversion temperature.

← Back to Chapter 27, “Heat Capacity, Enthalpy, and the Joule–Thomson Effect”

Chapter 28 The Carnot Cycle and the Efficiency of Heat Engines

Exercise 28.1: Absolute zero and the Carnot efficiency

Solution

The thermal efficiency of a Carnot cycle is $\eta=1-T_1/T_2$, where $T_2$ is the absolute temperature of the hot reservoir and $T_1$ is that of the cold reservoir. Fixing $T_2$ and considering the limit $T_1\to0$ (absolute zero), we obtain

$$ \lim_{T_1\to0}\eta=\lim_{T_1\to0}\left(1-\frac{T_1}{T_2}\right)=1-\frac{0}{T_2}=1 $$

so indeed $\eta\to1$ (an efficiency of 100%).

However, this does not mean that “if only we could prepare a reservoir at absolute zero, we could actually build a heat engine with 100% efficiency.” This limit merely describes the mathematical behavior of the formula when the variable $T_1$ is made to approach 0. According to the third law of thermodynamics (which we study in Chapter 32 of this text), it is physically impossible to reach absolute zero itself by any finite number of operations. Therefore every real cold reservoir has $T_1\gt0$, and $\eta=1-T_1/T_2$ is always strictly less than 1. The relation “$\eta\to1$ as $T_1\to0$” is a limit that shows a tendency: the lower the temperature of the cold reservoir is made, the higher the theoretical upper bound on the efficiency becomes. It does not mean that an efficiency of 100% can actually be achieved.

This is also consistent with the Kelvin–Planck statement in Section 28.1. The value $\eta=1$ corresponds to $Q_1=0$ (all the absorbed heat is converted into work without any heat being discarded to the cold reservoir), and the Kelvin–Planck statement forbids precisely this. The limit $T_1\to0$ does not mean that the cold reservoir disappears; it means that its temperature approaches absolute zero. By the third law, $T_1=0$ cannot be reached, so the limit $\eta\to1$ cannot be reached either, and the two do not contradict each other.

Exercise 28.2: Why the processes must be quasi-static

Solution

The four processes of a Carnot cycle must be quasi-static (sufficiently slow) in order to make the cycle a reversible process (a process that can be retraced in the opposite direction without leaving any trace on the surroundings). Being quasi-static is a necessary condition for reversibility; in addition, there must be no dissipation such as friction, and heat must be exchanged with the reservoirs without any temperature difference. If an intermediate process (for example, the adiabatic expansion) is carried out abruptly, nonuniformities (inhomogeneities) of pressure and temperature arise inside the gas, or dissipation such as turbulence and friction occurs, and the same path can no longer be retraced exactly in the opposite direction (the process becomes an irreversible process).

This reversibility was used in an essential way in the proof of Carnot's theorem (Section 28.4). In the proof, in order to compare with an arbitrary heat engine $\mathrm X$, we ran the reversible heat engine $\mathrm R$ (a Carnot cycle) backward, using the work produced by $\mathrm X$ directly to drive $\mathrm R$ as a refrigerator. If $\mathrm R$ contained irreversible processes, the operation of “running it backward” itself would be impossible in the first place (an irreversible process cannot be retraced in the opposite direction with the same amounts of heat and work flowing in and out; even if one forced it to run backward, the amounts of heat and work on the way back would differ from those on the way out, and the relation on which the proof is based—that the magnitudes on the way out and on the way back are the same—would break down). Therefore, if the processes of the Carnot cycle were not quasi-static but irreversible, the part of the proof in which we assumed that “$\mathrm R$ is run backward, with heat and work of the same magnitudes as on the way out flowing in and out” would not hold in the first place, and the whole proof would collapse. Quasi-staticity (reversibility) is an indispensable premise for Carnot's theorem to hold.

Exercise 28.3: Why the reversed Carnot cycle is a refrigerator

Solution

In the forward Carnot cycle, heat $Q_2$ is absorbed from the hot reservoir, heat $Q_1$ ($Q_1\lt Q_2$) is released to the cold reservoir, and the difference is extracted as the work $W'=Q_2-Q_1$ done by the system on the surroundings. When the cycle is run in reverse, all of these flows of heat and work change direction: heat $Q_1$ is absorbed from the cold reservoir, heat $Q_2$ is released to the hot reservoir, and for this purpose work $W_{\mathrm{in}}=Q_2-Q_1$ is supplied from the surroundings.

In other words, the reversed Carnot cycle does exactly what a refrigerator (which removes heat from the cold side, namely its interior) or a heat pump (which delivers heat to the hot side, the inside of a room) does: it “draws heat up from the cold side and discharges it to the hot side.” Unless work $W_{\mathrm{in}}$ is continually supplied from the surroundings, this pumping of heat does not occur—this is why a refrigerator or an air conditioner keeps consuming electricity (work).

This does not contradict the Clausius statement in Section 28.1 (“there is no process in which heat moves from a cold body to a hot body without the supply of work”). What the Clausius statement forbids is only the process in which heat moves spontaneously from cold to hot without the supply of work. In the reversed Carnot cycle, the “other change” of work $W_{\mathrm{in}}$ from the surroundings properly accompanies the transfer, so it is not a forbidden process.

Exercise 28.4: Differences between the Otto cycle and the Diesel cycle

Solution

The Otto cycle treats combustion (the process corresponding to heat absorption from outside) as an isochoric process. This is because in a gasoline engine the compressed air–fuel mixture is ignited in an instant by a spark from the spark plug, and combustion is considered to be complete before the piston has moved appreciably (only the pressure and temperature rise sharply while the volume stays almost constant). The Diesel cycle, on the other hand, treats combustion as an isobaric process. This is because a diesel engine has no spark plug: fuel is injected into air that has been compressed at a very high compression ratio and thereby heated, and combustion continues all the while the fuel is being injected as the piston is pushed out slowly, so the process can be regarded as one in which the volume increases while the pressure is kept almost constant.

This difference corresponds to a difference in the structure of actual engines. Because a gasoline engine (Otto cycle) uses forced ignition by a spark plug, raising the compression ratio too much makes “knocking,” in which the mixture ignites spontaneously before the spark, likely to occur, so there is an upper limit to the compression ratio (the Discussion in Section 28.6). A diesel engine (Diesel cycle), on the other hand, is designed from the outset to use self-ignition by the compression of air, so there is no need to worry about knocking, and a much higher compression ratio than in the Otto cycle ($r=15$–$25$ or so) can be realized. As we confirmed in Example 28.9, the Otto cycle has the higher efficiency when the two are compared at the same compression ratio, but since a diesel engine can have a higher compression ratio itself, it often achieves a better fuel economy (effective efficiency) than a gasoline engine in practice.

Exercise 28.5: Carnot's theorem and independence of the working substance

Solution
