By Carnot's theorem, the efficiency $\eta=1-T_1/T_2$ of a reversible engine operating between the same two reservoirs $T_1,T_2$ is a universal value determined only by the temperatures of the reservoirs, regardless of the working substance (an ideal gas, steam, or any other substance). This is a very strong statement. Conversely, it means that we can redefine “temperature” itself by using the “efficiency,” a quantity that can be measured without relying on the detailed properties of the working substance (such as the form of its equation of state).

Specifically, if the efficiency $\eta$ of a reversible engine operating between two reservoirs could be measured by an experiment (or, for the purposes of the definition, by a thought experiment), then the relation $\eta=1-T_1/T_2$ fixes the ratio of temperatures, $T_1/T_2=1-\eta$. This is entirely different from methods such as the “gas thermometer,” which defines temperature by relying on the properties of a particular substance (for example, the volume expansion of an ideal gas); it is a way of defining temperature that is common to all substances and based only on the efficiency of a heat engine. This is called the thermodynamic temperature scale (also called the absolute temperature scale; it is the original definition of the Kelvin scale; the content is the same as that of the Discussion box in Section 28.4). The fact that the efficiency $\eta=1-T_1/T_2$ of the ideal-gas Carnot cycle calculated in this chapter (where $T$ here is the temperature defined by the equation of state of an ideal gas) coincides with this universal thermodynamic temperature scale confirms that the definition of temperature using an ideal gas does not contradict the thermodynamic definition of temperature, which is independent of the working substance. This idea will serve as the foundation when we study entropy and the second law of thermodynamics more deeply in the following chapters.

Exercise 28.6: What $Q_2/T_2-Q_1/T_1=0$ suggests

Solution

A state quantity is a quantity whose value is determined solely by the present state (for example, the set of pressure, volume, and temperature), regardless of the path by which the substance arrived at that state (the internal energy that we learned about in Section 23.3 is the representative example). The quantity of heat $Q$ is in general not a state quantity in this sense—as we saw in this chapter, even after one full trip around a Carnot cycle we have $Q_2-Q_1\neq0$; “although the system has returned to its original state after one cycle, the net amount of heat that flowed in and out is not zero.” This means that the value of $Q$ depends on which processes (isothermal or adiabatic, along which path) were followed on the way, and this contradicts the definition of a state quantity.

However, as we confirmed in Section 28.5, when we compare $Q_2/T_2$ and $Q_1/T_1$, the quantities obtained by dividing each quantity of heat by the temperature of the reservoir with which it was exchanged, we get $Q_2/T_2-Q_1/T_1=0$, which returns exactly to zero after one cycle. Although $Q$ by itself depends on the path, with the combination $Q/T$ the changes over one cycle exactly cancel to zero—this strongly suggests the possibility that the quantity $S\equiv Q/T$ is, unlike $Q$, a state quantity determined only by the state (not by the path).

What kind of physical property, then, can we expect this new state quantity $S$ (entropy) to represent? The form $Q/T$ seems to express what fraction of the energy exchanged as heat has become a form that can never again be converted into work (heat that has merely been discarded to a low-temperature reservoir). Entropy is said to be a measure of how “disordered” the molecular motion of a system is (we shall study this in detail from Chapter 29 on). For example, if the system is operated reversibly, as in a Carnot cycle, the balance of $Q/T$ comes out exactly zero, but if irreversible processes (friction or abrupt changes) are mixed in along the way, this balance would break down and be biased toward an increase of $S$—this conjecture can be confirmed by learning the precise definition of entropy and its properties in the next chapter (Chapter 29).

Exercise 28.7: Carnot efficiency and actual efficiency of a thermal power plant

Solution

(1) For a hot reservoir at $T_2=800\ \mathrm{K}$ and a cold reservoir at $T_1=300\ \mathrm{K}$, the Carnot efficiency is

$$ \eta_{\mathrm{Carnot}}=1-\frac{T_1}{T_2}=1-\frac{300}{800}=1-0.375=0.625=62.5\,\% $$

(2) Since the actual thermal efficiency is $\eta=0.40$, its ratio to the Carnot efficiency is

$$ \frac{\eta}{\eta_{\mathrm{Carnot}}}=\frac{0.40}{0.625}=0.64 $$

That is, the actual efficiency is about 64% (a little over six-tenths) of the Carnot efficiency (the theoretical upper bound in this temperature range). Irreversibilities such as friction, heat losses, and heat exchange across temperature differences account for the loss of the remainder.

(3) Let the electric output (work per second) be $W'/t=1.0\times10^{9}\ \mathrm{W}=1.0\times10^{9}\ \mathrm{J/s}$. From $\eta=W'/Q_2$, the heat absorbed per second is

$$ \frac{Q_2}{t}=\frac{W'/t}{\eta}=\frac{1.0\times10^{9}\ \mathrm{J/s}}{0.40}=2.5\times10^{9}\ \mathrm{J/s}=2.5\ \mathrm{GW} $$

From $Q_1=Q_2-W'$, the heat discarded per second to the condenser is

$$ \frac{Q_1}{t}=2.5\times10^{9}-1.0\times10^{9}=1.5\times10^{9}\ \mathrm{J/s}=1.5\ \mathrm{GW} $$

Thus $60\,\%$ of the heat obtained from the fuel is discarded into the sea, rivers, or the atmosphere. (For reference: if the efficiency reached the Carnot efficiency of $62.5\,\%$, the heat required for the same electric output would be only $1.0\times10^{9}/0.625=1.6\times10^{9}\ \mathrm{J/s}$.)

Exercise 28.8: Comparison of the Otto cycle and the Carnot cycle

Solution

The thermal efficiency of the Otto cycle is $\eta=1-r^{1-\gamma}$. Substituting $r=10$ and $\gamma=1.4$, we obtain

$$ \eta_{\mathrm{Otto}}=1-10^{-0.4}=1-e^{-0.4\ln10}=1-e^{-0.921}\approx1-0.3981=0.602=60.2\,\% $$

The efficiency of a Carnot cycle operating between a maximum temperature of $2000\ \mathrm{K}$ and a minimum temperature of $300\ \mathrm{K}$ is

$$ \eta_{\mathrm{Carnot}}=1-\frac{300}{2000}=1-0.15=0.85=85\,\% $$

so the efficiency of the Otto cycle is the following fraction of the Carnot efficiency:

$$ \frac{0.602}{0.85}\approx0.708 $$

that is, about 71%. The reason the Otto cycle has a lower efficiency than the Carnot cycle is, as explained in the Discussion in Section 28.6, that the heat is absorbed not only from a reservoir at the maximum temperature $T_A$ but while the temperature rises from $T_D$ to $T_A$ (most of the heat is received at lower temperatures).

Exercise 28.9: Coefficient of performance of a freezer

Solution

The absolute temperatures are $T_1=-18+273.15=255.15\ \mathrm{K}$ (the inside of the freezer, the cold reservoir) and $T_2=27+273.15=300.15\ \mathrm{K}$ (the room, the hot reservoir).

(1) The coefficient of performance (COP) of the reversed Carnot cycle as a refrigerator is

$$ \mathrm{COP}_{\mathrm R}=\frac{T_1}{T_2-T_1}=\frac{255.15}{300.15-255.15}=\frac{255.15}{45.00}\approx5.67 $$

(2) The heat pumped out of the freezer per second is $Q_1/t=300\ \mathrm{J/s}$. From $\mathrm{COP}_{\mathrm R}=Q_1/W_{\mathrm{in}}$, the required power (the minimum electric power) is

$$ \frac{W_{\mathrm{in}}}{t}=\frac{Q_1/t}{\mathrm{COP}_{\mathrm R}}=\frac{300\ \mathrm{J/s}}{5.67}\approx52.9\ \mathrm{J/s}=52.9\ \mathrm{W} $$

(3) From $Q_2=Q_1+W_{\mathrm{in}}$, the heat released into the room per second is

$$ \frac{Q_2}{t}=300+52.9\approx3.53\times10^{2}\ \mathrm{J/s}=353\ \mathrm{W} $$

(Check: from $Q_2/Q_1=T_2/T_1$ we get $300\times300.15/255.15\approx353\ \mathrm{W}$, which agrees.) Because electric power is used to pump out the heat inside the freezer, more heat is released into the room than the heat carried out of the freezer. In an actual freezer, because of irreversibilities, the required electric power is larger than this ideal value.

Exercise 28.10: Work in the Otto cycle

Solution

Work in the adiabatic processes. Since $A\to B$ is an adiabatic process, $Q_{AB}=0$, and by the first law $\Delta U=Q-W'$ we have $W'_{AB}=-\Delta U_{AB}$. Since the change in the internal energy of an ideal gas is $\Delta U=C_V\Delta T$,

$$ W'_{AB}=-C_V(T_B-T_A)=C_V(T_A-T_B) $$

Similarly, since $C\to D$ is also an adiabatic process,

$$ W'_{CD}=-C_V(T_D-T_C)=C_V(T_C-T_D)\ (\lt0) $$

In the isochoric processes $D\to A$ and $B\to C$, $\dd V=0$, so the work is $0$. Therefore the net work in one cycle is

$$ W'=W'_{AB}+W'_{CD}=C_V(T_A-T_B)-C_V(T_D-T_C)=C_V(T_A-T_D)-C_V(T_B-T_C) $$

Here, as in Example 28.7, $Q_2=C_V(T_A-T_D)$ and $Q_1=C_V(T_B-T_C)$, so $W'=Q_2-Q_1$, which shows that the net work in one cycle equals $Q_2-Q_1$. (Furthermore, if we use $T_B=xT_A$, $T_C=xT_D$, and $x=r^{1-\gamma}$, then $W'=C_V(1-x)(T_A-T_D)=(1-r^{1-\gamma})Q_2$, and $\eta=W'/Q_2=1-r^{1-\gamma}$.)

Numerical values. $C_V=nR/(\gamma-1)=1.0\times8.314/0.4\approx20.8\ \mathrm{J/K}$. Since $x=8^{-0.4}\approx0.4353$,

$$ T_D=\frac{T_C}{x}=T_C\,r^{\gamma-1}=300\times8^{0.4}\approx300\times2.297\approx689\ \mathrm{K},\qquad T_B=xT_A=0.4353\times2000\approx871\ \mathrm{K} $$

Hence,

$$ W'_{AB}=20.8\times(2000-871)\approx2.35\times10^{4}\ \mathrm{J},\qquad W'_{CD}=20.8\times(300-689)\approx-8.09\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{J} $$ $$ W'=W'_{AB}+W'_{CD}\approx(2.35-0.81)\times10^{4}\ \mathrm{J}\approx1.54\times10^{4}\ \mathrm{J} $$

(Check: $Q_2=20.8\times(2000-689)\approx2.72\times10^{4}\ \mathrm{J}$ and $Q_1=20.8\times(871-300)\approx1.19\times10^{4}\ \mathrm{J}$, so $Q_2-Q_1\approx1.54\times10^{4}\ \mathrm{J}$. This also agrees with $W'/Q_2\approx0.565=1-8^{-0.4}$.)

Exercise 28.11: Checking numerically that the values of $Q/T$ agree

Solution

We have $n=1.0\ \mathrm{mol}$, $T_2=400\ \mathrm{K}$, $T_1=300\ \mathrm{K}$, $V_B/V_A=3.0/1.0=3.0$, $\ln3.0\approx1.0986$, and $R=8.314\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)}$. The heats in the isothermal processes are $Q_2=nRT_2\ln(V_B/V_A)$ and $Q_1=nRT_1\ln(V_B/V_A)$, so

$$ Q_2=1.0\times8.314\times400\times1.0986\approx3.65\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{J},\qquad Q_1=1.0\times8.314\times300\times1.0986\approx2.74\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{J} $$

Dividing these by the temperatures of the reservoirs with which the heat was exchanged, we get

$$ \frac{Q_2}{T_2}=\frac{3.65\times10^{3}}{400}\approx9.13\ \mathrm{J/K},\qquad\frac{Q_1}{T_1}=\frac{2.74\times10^{3}}{300}\approx9.13\ \mathrm{J/K} $$

and indeed they are equal (although $Q_2\neq Q_1$, we have $Q_2/T_2=Q_1/T_1$). In terms of formulas, this common value is $Q_2/T_2=nR\ln(V_B/V_A)$ and $Q_1/T_1=nR\ln(V_B/V_A)$, both of which equal $nR\ln(V_B/V_A)=1.0\times8.314\times1.0986\approx9.13\ \mathrm{J/K}$. The temperatures $T_2,T_1$ cancel and disappear, leaving only the volume ratio $V_B/V_A$—this is the numerical evidence that $Q/T$ can be a path-independent quantity (the entropy).

← Back to Chapter 28, “The Carnot Cycle and the Efficiency of Heat Engines”

Chapter 29 Entropy — Definition and Proof That It Is a State Quantity

Exercise 29.1: Entropy change in isothermal reversible compression

Solution

(1) Substitute $V_2=V_1/3$ into the result of Chapter 29 for an isothermal reversible change, $\Delta S=nR\ln(V_2/V_1)$:

$$ \Delta S_{\text{gas}}=nR\ln\frac{V_1/3}{V_1}=nR\ln\frac{1}{3}=-nR\ln3 $$

($\ln(1/3)=-\ln3\approx-1.0986$.) For $n=1\ \mathrm{mol}$,

$$ \Delta S_{\text{gas}}=-1\times8.314\times1.0986\ \mathrm{J/K}\approx-9.13\ \mathrm{J/K} $$

which is negative. Compression narrows the range of volume in which the gas molecules can move around, from $V_1$ to $V_1/3$, so the disorder of the molecular configurations decreases and the entropy decreases. This is exactly the opposite of what happened in free expansion, in which the volume expands and $\Delta S\gt0$.

(2) For an isothermal change of an ideal gas, $\Delta U=0$, so $Q=W'$, and $Q=nRT\ln(V_2/V_1)$.

$$ Q=1\times8.314\times300\times\ln\frac13\ \mathrm{J}\approx-2.74\times10^3\ \mathrm{J} $$

Since this is negative, the gas releases about $2.74\ \mathrm{kJ}$ of heat to the heat reservoir (consistent with $\Delta S_{\text{gas}}=Q/T=-2740/300\ \mathrm{J/K}\approx-9.13\ \mathrm{J/K}$).

(3) The heat reservoir receives the heat $2.74\ \mathrm{kJ}$ released by the gas at the temperature $T=300\ \mathrm{K}$, so

$$ \Delta S_{\text{reservoir}}=\frac{+2740\ \mathrm{J}}{300\ \mathrm{K}}\approx+9.13\ \mathrm{J/K},\qquad \Delta S_{\text{total}}=-9.13+9.13=0 $$

Thus, looking at the gas alone, the entropy has decreased, but the increase in the reservoir exactly cancels it. Since the change is reversible, the total is $\Delta S_{\text{total}}=0$, which does not violate the law of entropy increase ($\Delta S\ge0$ for the whole). If the same compression were carried out abruptly (irreversibly), the heat received by the reservoir would be more than $2.74\ \mathrm{kJ}$, and $\Delta S_{\text{total}}\gt0$.

Exercise 29.2: Why adiabatic free expansion is irreversible

Solution

The entropy change of the adiabatic free expansion found in Section 29.8 (volume $V_i\to V_f$, $V_f\gt V_i$) is

$$ \Delta S_{\text{fwd}}=nR\ln\frac{V_f}{V_i}\gt0 $$

The reverse process we now consider is the change in which, within the same adiabatic container, the gas returns spontaneously from volume $V_f$ to $V_i$ ($V_i\lt V_f$). Since entropy is a state quantity (Theorem 29.2), the entropy change of the reversed change is that of the original change with its sign reversed:

$$ \Delta S_{\text{bwd}}=nR\ln\frac{V_i}{V_f}=-nR\ln\frac{V_f}{V_i}=-\Delta S_{\text{fwd}}\lt0 $$

The whole adiabatic container (gas plus container) is an isolated system, and the law of entropy increase (Law 29.1) states that any change that can occur in an isolated system must satisfy $\Delta S\ge0$. In the reverse process $\Delta S_{\text{bwd}}\lt0$ (the entropy decreases), so this law forbids it. Therefore a change in which the expanded gas spontaneously returns to its original volume does not occur—this is the explanation, in terms of entropy, of why adiabatic free expansion is irreversible (does not proceed in the reverse direction). (In principle, the probability that all the gas molecules happen to gather in one half of the container at the same time is not zero, but for a number of molecules of the order of Avogadro's number that probability is astronomically small, and it is not observed in reality. Treating this “smallness of probability” quantitatively is the task of Boltzmann's relation $S=k_{\mathrm{B}}\ln W$ of statistical mechanics, which we study in Part VII.)

Exercise 29.3: Relation for the adiabatic processes of the Carnot cycle

Solution

The entropy changes of the two isothermal processes found in Section 29.5.1 are $\Delta S_{AB}=nR\ln(V_B/V_A)$ and $\Delta S_{CD}=nR\ln(V_D/V_C)$. By the property of logarithms $\ln x+\ln y=\ln(xy)$ (High School Mathematics, Chapter 6), their sum can be combined as

$$ \Delta S_{AB}+\Delta S_{CD}=nR\ln\frac{V_B}{V_A}+nR\ln\frac{V_D}{V_C}=nR\ln\left(\frac{V_B}{V_A}\cdot\frac{V_D}{V_C}\right) $$

Here, using $V_B/V_A=V_C/V_D$, which follows from the relation for adiabatic processes (Poisson's formula), the quantity inside the parentheses becomes

$$ \frac{V_B}{V_A}\cdot\frac{V_D}{V_C}=\frac{V_C}{V_D}\cdot\frac{V_D}{V_C}=1 $$

(If we replace $V_B/V_A$ by $V_C/V_D$, which is known to be equal to it, it becomes exactly the reciprocal of the remaining $V_D/V_C$, so their product is exactly $1$.) Therefore,

$$ \Delta S_{AB}+\Delta S_{CD}=nR\ln1=0 $$

so the two cancel exactly, with opposite signs. Physically, the relation $V_B/V_A=V_C/V_D$ means that the volume ratio of the isothermal expansion on the high-temperature side (by what factor the gas expanded) and the reciprocal of the volume ratio of the isothermal compression on the low-temperature side (by what factor it shrank) become exactly the same ratio through the adiabatic processes in between, and this symmetry of the ratio makes the entropy changes of the two isothermal processes equal in magnitude and opposite in sign.

Exercise 29.4: Why the law of entropy increase is limited to “adiabatic systems”

Solution

The Clausius inequality derived in Section 29.7 was, in general,

$$ \dd S\ge\frac{\dd'Q}{T} $$

Only in the case of an adiabatic system ($\dd'Q=0$) does the right-hand side become $0$, giving the simple form $\dd S\ge0$. For a system that exchanges heat with the outside ($\dd'Q\ne0$), the right-hand side $\dd'Q/T$ is not $0$, so in general the sign of $\dd S$ is not determined. In particular, consider the case where the system releases heat ($\dd'Q\lt0$): the right-hand side can be negative, so the inequality $\dd S\ge\dd'Q/T$ does not forbid $\dd S$ from being negative—that is, the entropy of that system alone decreasing.

As a concrete example, consider water freezing in a refrigerator. When water becomes ice, the water molecules line up in a more orderly crystal structure, so the entropy of the water (this system) decreases ($\Delta S_{\text{water}}\lt0$). Looking at the water alone, it seems that “the entropy is decreasing,” but the heat released by the water (the heat of solidification) is carried through the cooling apparatus of the refrigerator on to the outside air, where it increases the entropy of the environment by at least $|\Delta S_{\text{water}}|$. The total entropy summed over the water, the refrigerator, and the outside air satisfies

$$ \Delta S_{\text{total}}=\Delta S_{\text{water}}+\Delta S_{\text{env}}\ge0 $$

so there is no contradiction with Law 29.1 (the total is $=0$ for a reversible change and $\gt0$ for an irreversible one). That is, the law of entropy increase does not hold for every subsystem; it is a law that holds only for the whole that includes all the exchanges with the outside (which can be regarded as an adiabatic system). The entropy of a subsystem alone can be decreased from the outside through the flow of work and heat into and out of it (the refrigerator and the isothermal compression of Exercise 29.1 are examples)—but as the price, the entropy of the outside of the system (the environment) increases by just enough to make up for the system's decrease (exactly enough if the change is reversible, more than enough if it is irreversible).

Exercise 29.5: Significance of temperature measurement with a triple-point cell

Solution

A gas thermometer defines temperature on the basis of a property of a specific substance (a gas for which the ideal-gas approximation holds), namely the equation of state of an ideal gas $pV=nRT$ (or the Boyle–Charles law). Therefore, strictly speaking, the temperature value given by a gas thermometer may depend on “which gas was used” and on “how far the deviation from the ideal gas (such as the van der Waals correction) was corrected.”

In contrast, Theorem 29.1 (Carnot's theorem) guarantees that the efficiency $\eta=1-T_1/T_2$ of a reversible engine operating between two heat reservoirs takes exactly the same value whatever the working substance is (an ideal gas, a real gas, or any other substance). Therefore, once the triple point of water is fixed as the reference $T_2=273.16\ \mathrm{K}$, we need only operate a reversible engine (any working substance may be used) between it and a reservoir at an unknown low temperature $T_1$ and measure its efficiency $\eta$, and $T_1$ is determined uniquely from $T_1=(1-\eta)T_2$. The temperature defined by this method is a purely thermodynamic temperature (based only on the second law) that does not depend at all on the choice of the working substance, and it is called the thermodynamic temperature (absolute temperature).

At the triple point, the temperature and pressure at which the three phases ice, water, and water vapor can coexist are fixed to a single state ($273.16\ \mathrm{K}$, about $611.657\ \mathrm{Pa}$), so the same temperature can be reproduced accurately any number of times without adjusting the external pressure. This is its advantage as a reference point. The gas thermometer is widely used because in practice it approximates this thermodynamic temperature well (and becomes more accurate the closer it is brought to the ideal-gas limit), but as a definition in principle, the significance lies in the fact that the method based on Carnot's theorem gives a more fundamental definition of temperature, one that does not rely on the properties of a particular substance.

Exercise 29.6: Do “$Q/T$ is a state quantity” and “$Q$ itself is not a state quantity” contradict each other?

Solution

Whether an infinitesimal quantity $\dd X$ is an “infinitesimal change of a state quantity (a total differential)” is determined by whether, “when $\dd X$ is integrated along different paths with the initial and final states held fixed, the value is the same for every path.” If it is independent of the path, $X$ is a state quantity determined by the “present state” alone; if it depends on the path, $X$ is not a state quantity. For the two variables $(T,V)$, the condition for $\dd X=M\,\dd T+N\,\dd V$ to be a total differential follows from the fact that the second-order partial derivatives do not depend on the order of differentiation (Schwarz's theorem):

$$ \frac{\partial M}{\partial V}=\frac{\partial N}{\partial T} $$

This is the condition.

For an ideal gas ($n\ \mathrm{mol}$, $C_V$ constant), the infinitesimal quantity of heat in a reversible change is

$$ \dd'Q=C_V\,\dd T+\frac{nRT}{V}\,\dd V $$

Setting $M=C_V$ and $N=nRT/V$, we have

$$ \frac{\partial M}{\partial V}=0,\qquad\frac{\partial N}{\partial T}=\frac{nR}{V}\ne0 $$

which do not agree, so $\dd'Q$ is not a total differential and $Q$ is not a state quantity (this corresponds to the fact that in Example 29.4 the heats absorbed along the two paths differed, $5470\ \mathrm{J}$ and $7199\ \mathrm{J}$).

However, if we consider $\dd'Q/T$, obtained by multiplying this same $\dd'Q$ by $1/T$, we have

$$ \frac{\dd'Q}{T}=\frac{C_V}{T}\dd T+\frac{nR}{V}\dd V,\qquad \frac{\partial}{\partial V}\!\left(\frac{C_V}{T}\right)=0=\frac{\partial}{\partial T}\!\left(\frac{nR}{V}\right) $$

and the condition for a total differential holds. That is, $\dd'Q$ takes a value that changes with the path (it is not a state quantity), but $\dd'Q/T$, obtained by multiplying by $1/T$, becomes independent of the path (it becomes the infinitesimal change $\dd S$ of the state quantity $S$). A factor that, when multiplied by an infinitesimal quantity that is not a total differential, turns it into a total differential is called an integrating factor, and $1/T$ acts as an integrating factor of $\dd'Q$.

This is not a contradiction; it is merely that “the quantity $Q$ itself” and “the quantity obtained by multiplying $Q$ by $1/T$ and integrating ($S$)” are different quantities to begin with. The discussions in Sections 29.1 and 29.5 showed that $\oint\dd'Q/T=0$ holds likewise for a general working substance and a general reversible change. The general theory of integrating factors is studied in University Mathematics, Section 34.4 (Integrating Factors).

Exercise 29.7: Entropy of the isothermal expansion of a van der Waals gas

Solution

(1) Since the change is isothermal, use the formula $\Delta S=R\ln[(V_2-b)/(V_1-b)]$ of Chapter 29. With $V_1=0.50\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{m^3}$, $V_2=1.0\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{m^3}$, and $b=4.27\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{m^3}$,

$$ \frac{V_2-b}{V_1-b}=\frac{1.0\times10^{-3}-4.27\times10^{-5}}{0.50\times10^{-3}-4.27\times10^{-5}}=\frac{9.573\times10^{-4}}{4.573\times10^{-4}}\approx2.0934 $$ $$ \Delta S=8.314\times\ln2.0934\ \mathrm{J/K}\approx8.314\times0.7388\ \mathrm{J/K}\approx6.14\ \mathrm{J/K} $$

The ideal-gas value is $R\ln2=8.314\times0.6931\ \mathrm{J/K}\approx5.76\ \mathrm{J/K}$, so the deviation is $(6.14-5.76)/5.76\approx0.066$, that is, about $6.6\,\%$ larger.

(2) For $1.0\ \mathrm{L}\to2.0\ \mathrm{L}$ (Example 29.5), $\Delta S\approx5.95\ \mathrm{J/K}$, which was only about $3.2\,\%$ larger than the ideal-gas value. Therefore the effect of $b$ is larger for $0.50\ \mathrm{L}\to1.0\ \mathrm{L}$. The reason is that $b$ is a constant independent of the volume, so the smaller the volume (the larger the ratio $b/V$ of $b$ to $V$), the larger the reduction of the volume $V-b$ in which the gas can actually move around, and the volume ratio $(V_2-b)/(V_1-b)$ deviates from 2. In the limit of sufficiently large volume, $b/V\to0$ and the result approaches that of the ideal gas. Note that the attraction constant $a$ does not appear in $S$, so it has no effect on $\Delta S$ in either case.

Exercise 29.8: Entropy changes on heating water and on putting ice into water

Solution

(1) When water of mass $m=1000\ \mathrm{g}$ and specific heat $c=4.19\ \mathrm{J/(g\,K)}$ is heated reversibly, from $\dd'Q_{\text{rev}}=mc\,\dd T$ we obtain

$$ \Delta S=\int_{T_1}^{T_2}\frac{mc\,\dd T}{T}=mc\ln\frac{T_2}{T_1}=1000\times4.19\times\ln\frac{373.15}{293.15}\ \mathrm{J/K}\approx4190\times0.2415\ \mathrm{J/K}\approx1.01\times10^3\ \mathrm{J/K} $$

(2) The final temperature $T_f$ is determined by conservation of heat. The heat required for $100\ \mathrm{g}$ of ice to melt and warm up to $T_f$ is $100\times334+100\times4.19\times(T_f-273.15)$ [J], and the heat released by $1000\ \mathrm{g}$ of water cooling from $293.15\ \mathrm{K}$ to $T_f$ is $1000\times4.19\times(293.15-T_f)$ [J]; since the two are equal,

$$ 33400+419(T_f-273.15)=4190(293.15-T_f)\qquad\therefore\ T_f\approx284.09\ \mathrm{K}\ (\approx10.9\,^{\circ}\mathrm{C}) $$

Since the ice melts completely ($T_f\gt273.15\ \mathrm{K}$), the assumption is not contradicted. Because entropy is a state quantity, we calculate the entropy change by changing each part reversibly.

  • Melting of the ice ($334\ \mathrm{J/g}$ at $273.15\ \mathrm{K}$): $\Delta S_1=\dfrac{100\times334}{273.15}\ \mathrm{J/K}\approx122.3\ \mathrm{J/K}$
  • Heating of the melted water ($273.15\to284.09\ \mathrm{K}$): $\Delta S_2=419\times\ln\dfrac{284.09}{273.15}\ \mathrm{J/K}\approx419\times0.0393\ \mathrm{J/K}\approx16.4\ \mathrm{J/K}$
  • Cooling of the original water ($293.15\to284.09\ \mathrm{K}$): $\Delta S_3=4190\times\ln\dfrac{284.09}{293.15}\ \mathrm{J/K}\approx4190\times(-0.0314)\ \mathrm{J/K}\approx-131.6\ \mathrm{J/K}$

The total is $\Delta S=122.3+16.4-131.6\approx+7.1\ \mathrm{J/K}\gt0$. Since the change takes place inside an adiabatic container, the fact that the total entropy increases shows that this change is irreversible and proceeds spontaneously in this direction (a change in which ice melts into water and heat is transferred proceeds naturally, but the reverse does not).

← Back to Chapter 29, “Entropy — Definition and Proof That It Is a State Quantity”

Chapter 30 Free Energies — Direction of Change and Maximum Work

Exercise 30.1: Direction of heat transfer and the law of entropy increase

Solution

(1) The hot body ($T_2=350\ \mathrm{K}$) loses heat $Q=500\ \mathrm{J}$, and the cold body ($T_1=300\ \mathrm{K}$) receives it. Assuming that the temperature of each can be regarded as constant (the heat capacities being large compared with the quantity of heat),

$$ \Delta S_2=\frac{-Q}{T_2}=\frac{-500\ \mathrm{J}}{350\ \mathrm{K}}\approx-1.43\ \mathrm{J/K},\qquad \Delta S_1=\frac{Q}{T_1}=\frac{500\ \mathrm{J}}{300\ \mathrm{K}}\approx+1.67\ \mathrm{J/K} $$ $$ \Delta S_{\text{total}}=\Delta S_2+\Delta S_1\approx-1.4286+1.6667\approx+0.238\ \mathrm{J/K}\ (\gt0) $$

(2) Suppose that the same quantity of heat were transferred in the opposite direction (from the body at $300\ \mathrm{K}$ to the body at $350\ \mathrm{K}$). Every entropy change would then have the opposite sign, so $\Delta S_{\text{total}}\approx-0.238\ \mathrm{J/K}\lt0$. The two bodies taken together can be regarded as an isolated system if they are cut off from the outside by thermal insulation, so this contradicts the law of entropy increase for an isolated system, $\dd S\ge0$. Therefore, heat transfer in this direction does not occur spontaneously.

(3) In general, let $\dd'Q$ be the quantity of heat transferred from the hot body to the cold body. Then the total entropy change is $\dd S_{\text{total}}=\dd'Q\,(1/T_1-1/T_2)$, and since $T_2\gt T_1\gt0$, the quantity in parentheses is positive. For $\dd S_{\text{total}}\ge0$ we need $\dd'Q\ge0$; that is, heat is transferred only from hot to cold. This corresponds exactly to the Clausius statement of the second law of thermodynamics, “Heat does not pass spontaneously from a colder body to a hotter body.” The law of entropy increase is a unified, quantitative formulation of the various statements of the second law (the Clausius statement, the Thomson–Kelvin statement, and others), and when it is applied to the concrete phenomenon of the “direction of heat transfer,” the Clausius statement itself is obtained.

Exercise 30.2: The meaning of the discontinuous jumps in the $C_p/T$–$T$ graph

Solution

The vertical axis $C_p/T$ of the $C_p/T$–$T$ graph represents the rate of change of the entropy when the temperature is changed slightly at constant pressure, $(\partial S/\partial T)_p=C_p/T$. We reason in the following three steps, in this order.

(1) Inside each phase (whichever of solid, liquid, or gas), $C_p/T$ describes how the entropy grows as the temperature is raised gradually, and it varies continuously with temperature.

(2) Before and after the melting point $T_{\mathrm{fus}}$ and the boiling point $T_b$, the manner of thermal motion of the molecules (the degrees of freedom) differs from phase to phase, so the value of $C_p$ differs from phase to phase. As a result, the $C_p/T$ graph shows a step connecting the values of the phases on the two sides of each transition point.

(3) At the transition point itself, a finite amount of heat, the heat of fusion or heat of vaporization $\Delta_{\mathrm{trs}}H$, flows in or out while the temperature stays unchanged. Since the heat capacity is “heat ÷ temperature change,” if the temperature change is $0$ while the heat is finite, then $C_p$ is effectively infinite (a $\delta$-function-like divergence), and a thin peak rises in the $C_p/T$ graph. The area of this peak is $\displaystyle\int\frac{C_p}{T}\,\dd T=\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{trs}}H}{T_{\mathrm{trs}}}=\Delta_{\mathrm{trs}}S$ (Section 30.2). The $C_p/T$ graph in Figure 30.3 draws this peak as a thin line and draws the difference between the values of the phases on the two sides (the step).

In the $S$–$T$ graph, the area under the $C_p/T$ curve gives the increment of $S$, so in the regions of (1), $S$ increases smoothly, and the area of the peak in (3) appears directly as the stepwise jump $\Delta_{\mathrm{trs}}S$ of $S$ at $T_{\mathrm{trs}}$. The “step” in the $C_p/T$ graph represents the difference between the heat capacities of the phases, and the “jump” in the $S$ graph represents the effect of the latent heat itself; the same phase transition thus appears in different forms in the two graphs.

Exercise 30.3: Entropy change in heating and vaporizing water

Solution

(1) Heating stage: Since $C_{p,\mathrm{m}}$ is constant, the integral in the equation of Chapter 30 (the entropy change accompanying a temperature change at constant pressure) can be carried out, and

$$ \Delta S_1=\int_{298.15}^{373.15}\frac{C_{p,\mathrm{m}}}{T}\,\dd T=C_{p,\mathrm{m}}\ln\frac{373.15}{298.15}=75.3\times\ln(1.2516)\approx75.3\times0.2244\approx16.9\ \mathrm{J/(K\cdot mol)} $$

(2) Vaporization stage: This is a phase transition at constant temperature ($373.15\ \mathrm{K}$) and constant pressure, so from $\Delta S=\Delta H/T$,

$$ \Delta S_2=\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{vap}}H}{T_b}=\frac{40660\ \mathrm{J/mol}}{373.15\ \mathrm{K}}\approx109.0\ \mathrm{J/(K\cdot mol)} $$

(3) Total: Since entropy is a state quantity, the change for the whole process is simply the sum of the changes in the two stages:

$$ \Delta S=\Delta S_1+\Delta S_2\approx16.9+109.0\approx125.9\ \mathrm{J/(K\cdot mol)} $$

The vaporization stage is far larger than the heating stage. This is because, once the substance becomes a gas, the molecules can fly about freely and the disorder increases all at once; it is also consistent with Trouton's rule (Section 30.2).

Exercise 30.4: Why the Helmholtz free energy $A$ is the criterion for spontaneous change

Solution

We retrace the derivation in Section 30.4.1, separating it according to the conditions.

Where the condition “constant volume, no non-expansion work” was used: Since the volume does not change ($\dd V=0$) and there is no non-expansion work, the work done on the system is $\dd'W=-p\,\dd V+\dd'W_{\text{non-expansion}}=0$. From the first law of thermodynamics, $\dd U=\dd'Q+\dd'W$, we get $\dd U=\dd'Q$. Substituting this into the Clausius inequality $\dd S\ge\dd'Q/T$ gives $\dd S\ge\dd U/T$, that is,

$$ \dd U-T\,\dd S\le0 $$

This inequality was derived using only the condition “constant volume, no non-expansion work”; it is a general inequality that does not yet use the condition of constant temperature.

Where the condition “constant temperature” was used: Differentiating the definition of the Helmholtz free energy, $A=U-TS$, gives in general, by the product rule, $\dd A=\dd U-T\,\dd S-S\,\dd T$. If we now add the condition that the temperature is constant ($\dd T=0$), the third term vanishes and $\dd A=\dd U-T\,\dd S$.

Combining the two: The left-hand side of the inequality $\dd U-T\,\dd S\le0$ obtained from “constant volume, no non-expansion work” is, under the condition of “constant temperature,” exactly equal to $\dd A$. Therefore we obtain

$$ \dd A\le0 $$

In other words, $A$ serves as the criterion for spontaneous change only when all three conditions hold simultaneously: constant volume, constant temperature, and no non-expansion work. “Constant volume, no non-expansion work” plays the role of rewriting the Clausius inequality as an inequality in the variables of the system alone, and “constant temperature” plays the role of making $\dd U-T\,\dd S$ coincide with the total differential of $A$ itself.

Exercise 30.5: Work required for isothermal compression and $\Delta A$

Solution

(1) In quasi-static (reversible) isothermal compression, $p_{\mathrm{ex}}=p=nRT/V$. With $V_2=V_1/2$, the work done on the system is

$$ W=-\int_{V_1}^{V_1/2}p\,\dd V=-nRT\ln\frac{V_1/2}{V_1}=nRT\ln2=1\times8.314\times300\times0.6931\approx+1729\ \mathrm{J} $$

On the other hand, for an isothermal process of an ideal gas, $\Delta U=0$ and $\Delta S=nR\ln(V_2/V_1)=-nR\ln2$, so

$$ \Delta A=\Delta U-T\Delta S=+nRT\ln2\approx+1729\ \mathrm{J} $$

Since the change is reversible, $W=\Delta A$ (the equality in Law 30.4) holds.

(2) Let the initial pressure be $p_1=nRT/V_1$. Then the final pressure is $p_2=nRT/(V_1/2)=2p_1$, and “compressing all at once” means setting the external pressure to $p_{\mathrm{ex}}=2p_1$ from the start. Since the external pressure is constant, the work done on the system is

$$ W=-p_{\mathrm{ex}}(V_2-V_1)=-2p_1\left(\frac{V_1}{2}-V_1\right)=p_1V_1=nRT=8.314\times300\approx2494\ \mathrm{J} $$

The initial and final states are the same as in (1), so, since $A$ is a state quantity, $\Delta A$ is also the same, $+1729\ \mathrm{J}$, and $W=2494\ \mathrm{J}\ge\Delta A=1729\ \mathrm{J}$ holds. In an irreversible compression, more work is required than in the reversible case (equality holds only for a reversible change).

(3) For an ideal gas $\Delta U=0$, so by the first law $Q=-W=-2494\ \mathrm{J}$; that is, the system releases $2494\ \mathrm{J}$ of heat to the constant-temperature bath. The entropy change of the system is $\Delta S_{\text{sys}}=-nR\ln2\approx-5.76\ \mathrm{J/K}$, the entropy change of the constant-temperature bath (the surroundings) is $2494/300\approx+8.31\ \mathrm{J/K}$, and the total is

$$ \Delta S_{\text{total}}\approx-5.76+8.31\approx+2.55\ \mathrm{J/K}\ (\gt0) $$

Thus we can confirm that the entropy increases in an irreversible change. This also agrees with $(W-\Delta A)/T=(2494-1729)/300\approx2.55\ \mathrm{J/K}$. The extra work added compared with the reversible case corresponds to the amount dissipated as heat into the constant-temperature bath.

Exercise 30.6: The sign of the temperature dependence of the Gibbs energy

Solution

The relation $(\partial G/\partial T)_p=-S$ is a general relation obtained as follows. Substituting the first law for a quasi-static change, $\dd U=T\,\dd S-p\,\dd V$, into the total differential of $G=U+pV-TS$ gives $\dd G=-S\,\dd T+V\,\dd p$ (Section 30.6), and comparing coefficients with the total differential of $G(T,p)$, $\left(\partial G/\partial T\right)_p\dd T+\left(\partial G/\partial p\right)_T\dd p$, yields the relation. It does not depend on the kind of substance, nor on whether the substance is in the solid, liquid, or gas phase. (If we look at the definition $G=H-TS$ only at constant temperature, then $\dd T=0$, and the coefficient of $S$ cannot be read off.)

As in Section 30.3, the entropy $S$ on the right-hand side is expressed as $S(T)=\int_0^T(C_p/T)\,\dd T$ (with $S(0)=0$), and since the heat capacity $C_p\gt0$, we always have $S\gt0$ for $T\gt0$. Therefore $(\partial G/\partial T)_p=-S\lt0$; that is, when the temperature is raised at constant pressure, $G$ necessarily decreases.

If we compare the entropies of gas, liquid, and solid for the same substance at the same temperature, the gas, in which the molecules can fly about freely, has the largest, and the solid, in which the molecules are fixed in a regular lattice, has the smallest ($S_{\text{gas}}\gt S_{\text{liquid}}\gt S_{\text{solid}}$). For example, in the vaporization of water, $S$ increases by as much as $\Delta_{\mathrm{vap}}S\approx109\ \mathrm{J/(K\cdot mol)}$ (Section 30.2). Since the magnitude of $(\partial G/\partial T)_p=-S$ is determined by the magnitude of $S$, the decrease of $G$ with rising temperature is steepest for the gas and gentlest for the solid. The more degrees of freedom of molecular motion a phase has, the more easily it “earns entropy,” and the more $G$ can be lowered as the temperature rises. As a result, the gas is selected as the phase with the lowest $G$ at high temperatures, and the solid at low temperatures.

Exercise 30.7: The Daniell cell and the maximum electrical work

Solution

(1) The electrical work that can be extracted when the cell is operated reversibly (the maximum non-expansion work) is $nFE^\circ$, and this equals $-\Delta G^\circ$ (Law 30.5). Therefore,

$$ \Delta G^\circ=-nFE^\circ=-2\times96485\ \mathrm{C/mol}\times1.10\ \mathrm{V}\approx-2.12\times10^{5}\ \mathrm{J/mol}=-212\ \mathrm{kJ/mol} $$

(since $\mathrm{C\cdot V}=\mathrm{J}$, the unit is $\mathrm{J/mol}$).

(2) The amount of substance in $1.00\ \mathrm{g}$ of zinc is $1.00/65.38\approx1.53\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{mol}$, so the maximum electrical work that can be extracted is

$$ -\Delta G^\circ\times1.53\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{mol}\approx2.123\times10^{5}\times1.53\times10^{-2}\approx3.25\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{J}=3.25\ \mathrm{kJ} $$

In an actual cell, because of irreversibilities such as internal resistance, the work that can be extracted is smaller than this.

Exercise 30.8: Temperature range in which a reaction proceeds spontaneously

Solution

Since the process is at constant pressure and constant temperature (with no non-expansion work), the reaction proceeds spontaneously when $\Delta G\lt0$ (Law 30.3). Assuming that $\Delta H,\Delta S$ do not depend on temperature,

$$ \Delta G=\Delta H-T\Delta S=-50000\ \mathrm{J/mol}-T\times(-100\ \mathrm{J/(K\cdot mol)})=-50000+100\,T\ \ (\mathrm{J/mol}) $$

The condition $\Delta G\lt0$ is $100\,T\lt50000$, that is, $T\lt500\ \mathrm{K}$. At $T=500\ \mathrm{K}$, $\Delta G=0$ and the system is at equilibrium (the forward and reverse reactions balance); for $T\gt500\ \mathrm{K}$, $\Delta G\gt0$ and the reverse reaction becomes spontaneous. Hence the temperature range in which the reaction proceeds spontaneously is $0\lt T\lt500\ \mathrm{K}$. For example, at $T=300\ \mathrm{K}$ we have $\Delta G=-20\ \mathrm{kJ/mol}$, and at $T=600\ \mathrm{K}$ it becomes $+10\ \mathrm{kJ/mol}$.

Looking at the roles of the two terms of $\Delta G=\Delta H-T\Delta S$: the term with $\Delta H\lt0$ (exothermic) always favors the reaction, but when $\Delta S\lt0$ (a reaction in which the arrangement of the molecules loses disorder), $-T\Delta S=+100\,T$ is positive and grows larger at higher temperatures, which disfavors the reaction. At high temperatures the term for the loss of disorder outweighs the benefit of the heat release, so even with $\Delta H\lt0$ the reaction does not proceed. This can also be understood from the viewpoint of Section 30.4 (whether the decrease in the entropy of the system can be compensated by the increase in the entropy of the surroundings, $-\Delta H/T$, caused by the heat $-\Delta H$ released to the surroundings). The higher the temperature, the smaller the increase in the entropy of the surroundings, $-\Delta H/T$, for the same heat received.

← Back to Chapter 30, “Free Energies — Direction of Change and Maximum Work”

Chapter 31 Thermodynamic Relations and the Maxwell Relations

Exercise 31.1: Rederiving Maxwell relation (IV)

Solution

We regard the Gibbs energy (Gibbs free energy) $G=H-TS$ as a function of the pressure $p$ and the temperature $T$. As verified in Section 31.4 of this chapter, its total differential is

$$ \dd G=V\,\dd p-S\,\dd T $$

On the other hand, the general formula for the total differential of $G=G(p,T)$ is

$$ \dd G=\left(\frac{\partial G}{\partial p}\right)_T\dd p+\left(\frac{\partial G}{\partial T}\right)_p\dd T $$

and since $\dd p,\dd T$ are infinitesimal quantities that can be varied independently, comparing the coefficients gives

$$ V=\left(\frac{\partial G}{\partial p}\right)_T,\qquad -S=\left(\frac{\partial G}{\partial T}\right)_p $$

Differentiating the first equation partially with respect to $T$ (holding $p$ constant), we obtain

$$ \left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_p=\frac{\partial^2G}{\partial T\,\partial p} $$

Differentiating the second equation partially with respect to $p$ (holding $T$ constant), we obtain

$$ -\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial p}\right)_T=\frac{\partial^2G}{\partial p\,\partial T} $$

Since $G$, as a state quantity, is a smooth function (one that can be partially differentiated any number of times), Schwarz's theorem gives $\dfrac{\partial^2G}{\partial T\,\partial p}=\dfrac{\partial^2G}{\partial p\,\partial T}$. Therefore the right-hand sides are equal, and so the left-hand sides are equal as well:

$$ \left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_p=-\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial p}\right)_T $$

This is Maxwell relation (IV) of Chapter 31, and we have obtained exactly the same result as in the derivation in the main text (Section 31.4).

Exercise 31.2: The internal pressure of a gas that accounts only for the excluded volume

Solution

We substitute the equation of state $p=\dfrac{nRT}{V-nb}$ into the thermodynamic equation of state of the main text,

$$ \pi_T=\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial V}\right)_T=T\left(\frac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_V-p $$

First, to find $\left(\dfrac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_V$ we only need to differentiate partially with respect to $T$ while holding $V,n,b$ constant, so

$$ \left(\frac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_V=\frac{nR}{V-nb} $$

Substituting this into the expression for $\pi_T$, we get

$$ \pi_T=T\cdot\frac{nR}{V-nb}-p=\frac{nRT}{V-nb}-\frac{nRT}{V-nb}=0 $$

The two terms on the right-hand side cancel exactly, and we obtain $\pi_T=0$.

This result is exactly the same “zero internal pressure” as for an ideal gas. Physically, since this is a model with $a=0$ (neglecting intermolecular forces), no work is needed to pull the molecules apart, and therefore the internal energy does not change when the volume is changed isothermally. On the other hand, the internal pressure of the van der Waals gas found in Section 31.6 of this chapter, $\pi_T=an^2/V^2$, arises precisely from the attraction term $a$ alone—that is, this exercise shows that the cause of $\pi_T\neq0$ is “intermolecular forces (attraction),” not “the size of the molecules themselves (excluded volume).” In the equation of state, the effect of the excluded volume merely replaces the volume by $V-nb$, so the structure is such that the effect of $b$ cancels out cleanly upon partial differentiation with respect to $T$.

Exercise 31.3: Equation of state and internal energy of a photon gas (black-body radiation)

Solution

(a) The entropy of a photon gas is $S=\dfrac43aVT^3$ (here $a$ is the radiation constant, and the entropy is a function of $V,T$). Differentiating this partially with respect to $V$ (holding $T$ constant), we obtain

$$ \left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial V}\right)_T=\frac43aT^3 $$

Substituting this into Maxwell relation (III), $\left(\dfrac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_V=\left(\dfrac{\partial S}{\partial V}\right)_T$, gives

$$ \left(\frac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_V=\frac43aT^3 $$

Integrating the pressure $p$ with respect to $T$ while holding $V$ constant, we obtain

$$ p=\int\frac43aT^3\,\dd T=\frac43a\cdot\frac{T^4}{4}+f(V)=\frac13aT^4+f(V) $$

($f(V)$ is the constant of integration—but since the integral is taken with $V$ held fixed, it is a “constant” that may depend on $V$ but not on $T$). Now, as a physical boundary condition, at absolute zero $T\to0$ no photons (thermal radiation) exist, so the pressure must also be $0$: $p(V,0)=0$. Substituting this into the equation above gives $0=0+f(V)$, hence $f(V)=0$ (for every $V$). Therefore

$$ p=\frac13aT^4 $$

The fact that the pressure is determined by the temperature alone, independently of the volume $V$ ($\left(\partial p/\partial V\right)_T=0$), is also a property peculiar to the photon gas, in contrast to the ideal gas and the van der Waals gas seen in the main text (in both of which $p$ depends on $V$)—because a photon gas is, in place of “molecules,” a collection of electromagnetic waves with a fixed energy density independent of the size of the box.

(b) Substituting $p=\dfrac13aT^4$ obtained in (a) and $\left(\dfrac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_V=\dfrac43aT^3$ into the thermodynamic equation of state (Theorem 31.5), $\left(\dfrac{\partial U}{\partial V}\right)_T=T\left(\dfrac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_V-p$, we obtain

$$ \left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial V}\right)_T=T\cdot\frac43aT^3-\frac13aT^4=aT^4 $$

Integrating this with respect to $V$ while holding $T$ constant gives $U=aT^4V+f(T)$ ($f(T)$ is a constant of integration independent of $V$). In a photon gas the photons disappear and $U\to0$ as $V\to0$, so $f(T)=0$, and therefore

$$ U=aVT^4 $$

(energy density $u=U/V=aT^4$. Unit check: $\mathrm{J\,m^{-3}\,K^{-4}}\times\mathrm{m^3}\times\mathrm K^4=\mathrm J$.) As a check, using $S=\dfrac43aVT^3$ and $p=\dfrac13aT^4$ we have $T\,\dd S-p\,\dd V=\dfrac43aT^4\dd V+4aVT^3\dd T-\dfrac13aT^4\dd V=aT^4\dd V+4aVT^3\dd T$, which agrees with $\dd(aVT^4)$.

Exercise 31.4: Direct verification of the triple product rule

Solution

We solve the equation of state of an ideal gas, $pV=nRT$, for each variable in turn.

(i) Differentiating $p=nRT/V$ partially with respect to $T$ (holding $V$ constant), we obtain

$$ \left(\frac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_V=\frac{nR}{V} $$

(ii) Differentiating $T=pV/(nR)$ partially with respect to $V$ (holding $p$ constant), we obtain

$$ \left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial V}\right)_p=\frac{p}{nR} $$

(iii) Differentiating $V=nRT/p$ partially with respect to $p$ (holding $T$ constant), we obtain

$$ \left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial p}\right)_T=-\frac{nRT}{p^2} $$

Multiplying these three together, we get

$$ \left(\frac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_V\left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial V}\right)_p\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial p}\right)_T =\frac{nR}{V}\times\frac{p}{nR}\times\left(-\frac{nRT}{p^2}\right) =\frac{p}{V}\times\left(-\frac{nRT}{p^2}\right)=-\frac{nRT}{pV} $$

Since the equation of state $pV=nRT$ gives $nRT/(pV)=1$, the product is $-1$. We have thus confirmed that the cyclic relation (triple product rule) $\left(\dfrac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_V\left(\dfrac{\partial T}{\partial V}\right)_p\left(\dfrac{\partial V}{\partial p}\right)_T=-1$ indeed holds.

Exercise 31.5: Why a negative isothermal compressibility does not occur

Solution

As we saw in Section 31.8.2 of this chapter, $\left(\dfrac{\partial^2A}{\partial V^2}\right)_T=\dfrac{1}{\kappa_TV}$. If $\kappa_T\lt0$ were to hold over some range of volumes, this second partial derivative would be negative; that is, $A(T,V)$ (as a function of $V$ at constant temperature $T$) would be concave down over that range.

On the graph of a concave-down function, every point in the interior of the region is a candidate for a “local maximum,” not a “local minimum.” However, as we confirmed in Section 31.8.2, a stable equilibrium state at constant volume and constant temperature must be a state in which the Helmholtz energy $A$ is at a local minimum (Law 30.2: the system changes spontaneously in the direction $\dd A\le0$). On an interval where $A(V)$ is concave down, no point can be a local minimum, so the system cannot remain there—with only a slight change of volume, it moves spontaneously in the direction in which $A$ can be lowered still further (toward a neighboring, more stable state).

Specifically, in a region with $\kappa_T\lt0$ we have $\left(\partial p/\partial V\right)_T\gt0$ (the pressure rises when the volume is increased), so a part whose volume has become slightly larger acquires a pressure higher than its surroundings, pushes the surroundings back, and tries to expand further—conversely, a part whose volume has become smaller has its pressure lowered, is pushed by the surroundings, and tries to contract further. Fluctuations in either direction are amplified (positive feedback), so a uniform state cannot be maintained, and in the end the system separates into a part with large volume (low density = gas) and a part with small volume (high density = liquid). Therefore the region with $\kappa_T\lt0$ is not physically realized, and real substances avoid that region and take a state separated into two phases (gas and liquid), that is, gas–liquid coexistence.

Exercise 31.6: Why are the natural variables of the Helmholtz energy $(T,V)$?

Solution

According to Definition 31.1 (natural variables), natural variables are a pair of independent variables $(x,y)$ such that one can write $\dd\Phi=X\,\dd x+Y\,\dd y$ and, moreover, the coefficients $X,Y$ are basic quantities, each being one of $T,S,p,V$ (up to sign). As we derived in Section 31.3 of this chapter, the total differential of the Helmholtz energy is

$$ \dd A=-p\,\dd V-S\,\dd T $$

and the coefficients of $\dd V$ and $\dd T$ are the basic quantities $-p$ and $-S$, respectively. Therefore the natural variables of $A$ are $(T,V)$. In this case $p=-\left(\dfrac{\partial A}{\partial V}\right)_T$ and $S=-\left(\dfrac{\partial A}{\partial T}\right)_V$ are obtained directly as first partial derivatives of $A(T,V)$, and $U=A+TS$, $H=U+pV$, $G=A+pV$ can also be found by differentiation and addition alone, so knowing the single function $A(T,V)$ gives all the thermodynamic information about the system.

By contrast, if we forcibly write $A$ as a function of $(T,p)$, substituting $\dd V=\left(\dfrac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_p\dd T+\left(\dfrac{\partial V}{\partial p}\right)_T\dd p$ gives

$$ \dd A=-\Bigl(S+p\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_p\Bigr)\dd T-p\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial p}\right)_T\dd p $$

The coefficients $S+p\left(\dfrac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_p$ and $-p\left(\dfrac{\partial V}{\partial p}\right)_T$ are complicated quantities different from any of $T,S,p,V$, and $S,p$ cannot be read off directly from the first partial derivatives. In other words, $(T,p)$ are not natural variables of $A$.

When we want a potential whose natural variables are $(T,p)$, we perform the Legendre transformation (Definition 31.2, Table 31.2) that replaces the independent variable $V$ of $A(T,V)$ by $p$, the conjugate variable $-p$ with its sign changed. Subtracting (conjugate variable) $\times$ (variable) from $A$ gives $A-\left(\dfrac{\partial A}{\partial V}\right)_TV=A+pV$, and $A+pV=U-TS+pV=H-TS=G$, that is, the Gibbs energy. Thus the correct answer to the demand “I want to treat $A$ as a function of $(T,p)$” is not to “forcibly make $A$ a function of $(T,p)$,” but to “switch to another state quantity $G$ whose natural variables are $(T,p)$.” Since the Helmholtz energy and the Gibbs energy have different natural variables, both are needed.

Exercise 31.7: $C_p-C_V$ of aluminum

Solution

Using Formula 31.5 per $1\ \mathrm{mol}$, we have

$$ C_{p,\mathrm m}-C_{V,\mathrm m}=\frac{\alpha^2TV_{\mathrm m}}{\kappa_T} =\frac{(6.9\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{K^{-1}})^2(300\ \mathrm K)(1.0\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{m^3/mol})}{1.4\times10^{-11}\ \mathrm{Pa^{-1}}} $$

The numerator is $(4.76\times10^{-9})(300)(1.0\times10^{-5})=1.43\times10^{-11}$ (in units of $\mathrm{K^{-1}\,m^3\,mol^{-1}}$), and dividing this by $1.4\times10^{-11}\ \mathrm{Pa^{-1}}$ gives

$$ C_{p,\mathrm m}-C_{V,\mathrm m}\approx1.0\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)} $$

(Unit check: $\mathrm{K^{-1}\,m^3\,mol^{-1}\,Pa}=\mathrm{K^{-1}\,J\,mol^{-1}}$.) Relative to $C_{p,\mathrm m}\approx24\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)}$, the ratio is $1.0/24\approx0.04$, that is, about $4\ \%$. As with copper (about $3\ \%$), the difference between $C_p$ and $C_V$ is small for solids.

Exercise 31.8: Temperature rise of copper in adiabatic compression

Solution

Regarding $\alpha,V,C_p$ in $\left(\dfrac{\partial T}{\partial p}\right)_S=\dfrac{\alpha TV}{C_p}$ as constant, we have $\Delta T\approx\dfrac{\alpha TV_{\mathrm m}}{C_{p,\mathrm m}}\Delta p$. Substituting the numerical values gives

$$ \Delta T=\frac{(5.0\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{K^{-1}})(300\ \mathrm K)(7.1\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{m^3/mol})}{24\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)}}\times1.0\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{Pa} =\frac{1.07\times10^{-7}}{24}\times10^{8}\ \mathrm K=0.44\ \mathrm K $$

(Unit check: $\mathrm{m^3\,Pa/(J/K)}=\mathrm K$.) The temperature rises by about $0.44\ \mathrm K$.

Next, $C_p\Delta T=24\ \mathrm{J/K}\times0.44\ \mathrm K=1.07\times10^{1}\ \mathrm J\approx11\ \mathrm J$, which equals the heat released during the isothermal compression in Example 31.6, $|Q|=T|\Delta S|=T\alpha V_{\mathrm m}\Delta p\approx11\ \mathrm J$. The reason is as follows. In an adiabatic process the entropy is constant, so the decrease in entropy due to the compression, $-\alpha V\Delta p$ (Formula 31.4), is canceled by the increase in entropy due to the temperature rise, $C_p\Delta T/T$: $C_p\Delta T/T=\alpha V\Delta p$. In other words, “the temperature rise in adiabatic compression multiplied by $C_p$” equals “the heat released in isothermal compression.”
