Chapter 32 The Third Law of Thermodynamics and Rules for Phase Transitions

Exercise 32.1: Explaining the third law in your own words

Solution

The third law of thermodynamics (Sommerfeld's formulation) can be explained as follows: “As the temperature of a substance is brought closer to absolute zero, the entropy of that substance (a quantity expressing the disorder of the arrangement and motion of its molecules and atoms) approaches a certain definite value $S_0$. Moreover, this $S_0$ does not depend on how much pressure is applied to the substance, nor on what state of aggregation the substance is in (gas, liquid, solid, or a difference in crystal structure).”

This statement is broader than “the entropy change of a reaction, $\Delta S\to0$” (Nernst's theorem, Law 32.1) in the following respect. Nernst's theorem states only that, for substances A and B in condensed phases that are linked by a reaction, $S_{\mathrm A}$ and $S_{\mathrm B}$ approach the same value as $T\to0$ ($\Delta S=S_{\mathrm B}-S_{\mathrm A}\to0$); it says nothing about what that common value is (whether it may be set equal to $0$), nor about the relation between the entropies of substances that are not linked by a reaction, or of states that differ in pressure or state of aggregation. By contrast, the third law (Sommerfeld's formulation) asserts that the entropy $S$ of each individual substance itself approaches a common value $S_0$ that is independent of pressure and state of aggregation, and this makes it possible to consider an absolute entropy, with $T=0$ as the reference, for any substance and any state.

Exercise 32.2: Residual entropy of glass

Solution

The third law (in its stricter form, including Planck's remark) guarantees $S_0=0$ only when the substance is a “pure crystal,” that is, when the atoms or molecules are arranged in exactly one, perfectly regular configuration. In the language of statistical mechanics, this corresponds to the number of microscopic states the substance can take at absolute zero (the thermodynamic weight) being $W=1$ (effectively only one way of arranging the configuration), so that $S=k_{\mathrm B}\ln W=k_{\mathrm B}\ln1=0$.

Glass (an amorphous solid), however, is made by cooling a liquid rapidly, so the disorder of the atomic and molecular arrangement that existed in the liquid state is “frozen in” as it is, without time to crystallize (to rearrange into a regular pattern). That is, even when $T=0$ is reached, many possible patterns ($W\gt1$) of atomic and molecular arrangements still remain, and $S(0)=k_{\mathrm B}\ln W\gt0$ takes a positive value (the residual entropy). This is because glass is not in a “true equilibrium state” in the thermodynamic sense but in a nonequilibrium state in which the arrangement has happened to stall, and it therefore falls outside the “pure crystal” condition that the third law assumes.

Exercise 32.3: Back-calculating the boiling point of methane from Trouton's rule

Solution

Solving Trouton's rule $\Delta_{\mathrm{vap}}S=\Delta_{\mathrm{vap}}H/T_b\approx85\ \mathrm{J\,K^{-1}mol^{-1}}$ for $T_b$ gives

$$ T_b\approx\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{vap}}H}{85\ \mathrm{J\,K^{-1}mol^{-1}}}=\frac{8190\ \mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}}{85\ \mathrm{J\,K^{-1}mol^{-1}}}\approx96.4\ \mathrm K $$

Compared with the measured boiling point of $111.7\ \mathrm K$, the estimate is about $13.7\%$ lower than the measured value. The entropy of vaporization calculated backward from the measured data is

$$ \Delta_{\mathrm{vap}}S=\frac{8190\ \mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}}{111.7\ \mathrm K}\approx73.3\ \mathrm{J\,K^{-1}mol^{-1}} $$

which is considerably smaller than the Trouton guideline of $85\ \mathrm{J\,K^{-1}mol^{-1}}$. That is, the $\Delta_{\mathrm{vap}}S$ of methane is about $14\ \%$ smaller than the guideline value of $85$ (the amber point in Figure 32.5). Methane is a small, symmetric, nonpolar molecule with no strong intermolecular interactions such as hydrogen bonds; it has a low boiling point ($111.7\ \mathrm K$) and can already move around fairly freely even while still a liquid, so the increase on becoming a gas is presumably smaller. However, the reason for this deviation is not simple, and it is best to take Trouton's rule as “a guideline good to roughly $\pm$ 10–20%.”

Exercise 32.4: Slope of the sublimation curve of ice

Solution

Substituting the ideal-gas approximation $\Delta V\approx V_{\text{vapor}}=RT/p$ into the Clausius–Clapeyron equation $\dd p/\dd T=\Delta H/(T\Delta V)$ gives

$$ \frac{\dd p}{\dd T}=\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{sub}}H}{T\times(RT/p)}=\frac{p\,\Delta_{\mathrm{sub}}H}{RT^2} $$

Substituting the values at the triple point, $T_{\mathrm{tp}}=273.16\ \mathrm K$, $p_{\mathrm{tp}}=611.657\ \mathrm{Pa}$, $\Delta_{\mathrm{sub}}H=51060\ \mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}$, and $R=8.314\ \mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}K^{-1}}$, gives

$$ \frac{\dd p}{\dd T}=\frac{611.657\times51060}{8.314\times273.16^2}\approx\frac{3.1233\times10^7}{6.2036\times10^5}\approx50.3\ \mathrm{Pa/K} $$

This value is positive (the same behavior as ordinary substances: raising the pressure also raises the temperature at which sublimation occurs). Compared with the slope of the melting curve found in Example 32.9, $\dd p/\dd T\approx-13.47\ \mathrm{MPa/K}=-1.347\times10^7\ \mathrm{Pa/K}$, we note the following.

  • Sign: the sublimation curve has a positive slope and the melting curve a negative one—water (together with gallium, bismuth, silicon, germanium, and others) has a melting curve whose slope is opposite in direction to that of ordinary substances.
  • Magnitude: the slope of the sublimation curve ($\sim50\ \mathrm{Pa/K}$) is smaller than the magnitude of the slope of the melting curve ($\sim1.3\times10^7\ \mathrm{Pa/K}$) by a factor of about 270,000 (more than five orders of magnitude). This is because in sublimation the volume change $\Delta V$ of the gas is very large (the denominator is large), whereas in melting there is only a slight volume change between the two condensed phases, solid and liquid (the denominator is very small).

At the triple point, the three curves—the sublimation curve, the melting curve, and the vaporization curve—meet at exactly one point, forming the triple point of the $p$-$T$ phase diagram of water.

Exercise 32.5: Explaining with a diagram why absolute zero cannot be reached

Solution

By the third law, the two entropy curves in Figure 32.7, $S(T,B=0)$ (no magnetic field) and $S(T,B=B_1)$ (with a magnetic field), both converge to the same value $S_0$ as $T\to0$. On the other hand, for $T\gt0$ the $B_1$ curve lies below the $B=0$ curve, and the gap between the two approaches $0$ as $T\to0$. One cycle of adiabatic demagnetization (isothermal magnetization followed by adiabatic demagnetization) corresponds to one step of a staircase that goes back and forth between these two curves: by isothermal magnetization we go from the $B=0$ curve to the $B_1$ curve without changing the temperature (lowering the entropy), and by adiabatic demagnetization we go from the $B_1$ curve to the $B=0$ curve without changing the entropy (lowering the temperature).

Suppose, hypothetically, that the two curves approached different values as $T\to0$, for example $S_0(B_1)\lt S_0(0)$. Then the adiabatic-demagnetization line running horizontally from a point with entropy lower than $S_0(0)$ would reach $T=0$ without crossing the $B=0$ curve anywhere. That is, $T=0$ would be reached in a finite number of operations. But the third law requires the two curves to converge to the same value $S_0$ at $T=0$, so a line running horizontally from a point with $T\gt0$ always meets the $B=0$ curve at a temperature $T'\lt T$ that is still higher than $T=0$. Moreover, since the gap between the curves becomes small as $T\to0$, the temperature drop achieved in a single step also becomes small (in the model of Figure 32.7(a), the temperature merely halves with each step). Therefore, no matter how many times the process is repeated, the temperature never exactly coincides with $T=0$, and reaching it would require an infinite number of operations. This is why the third law leads to the unattainability of absolute zero. Note also that since $C_p\to0$ as $T\to0$, even a slight heat leak raises the temperature, so keeping the temperature low is also difficult (Exercise 32.8).

Exercise 32.6: Which holds better, Trouton's rule or Richards's rule?

Solution

In vaporization, in which a liquid becomes a gas, the molecules go from a state in which they were immediately adjacent to one another to a state in which they can fly freely about the whole space of the container, so the degrees of freedom of their motion change dramatically. At the boiling point at 1 atm, the volume increases by a factor of roughly $10^2\sim10^3$, and the increase in the entropy of translational motion due to this “expansion of the space in which the molecules can move freely” is of roughly similar magnitude regardless of the substance (for example, for a 300-fold increase in volume, $R\ln300\approx47\ \mathrm{J\,K^{-1}mol^{-1}}$). For that reason, the entropy change accompanying vaporization tends to take very similar values for all liquids. This is why Trouton's rule holds well.

In melting, by contrast, in which a solid becomes a liquid, only the regularity of the positional relationships among the molecules (the periodic arrangement of the crystal) is lost, and the extent of the space in which each molecule can move freely does not change as dramatically as in vaporization. For that reason, the entropy change accompanying melting strongly reflects the individual circumstances of each substance: how regular the original crystal structure was, in what manner the molecules were bonded to one another (the presence or absence of hydrogen bonds, and so on), and whether the disorder of molecular orientation newly increases. As a result, Richards's rule, $\Delta_{\mathrm{fus}}S\approx R$, is adequate for simple substances such as metals, but it deviates greatly for molecular crystals such as ice ($22.0\ \mathrm{J\,K^{-1}mol^{-1}}$) and benzene (about $35\ \mathrm{J\,K^{-1}mol^{-1}}$), and so it does not hold as well as Trouton's rule.

Exercise 32.7: Why was the idea that “$\Delta G\to\Delta H$ is obvious” insufficient?

Solution

Looking only at the equation $\Delta G=\Delta H-T\Delta S$, as long as $\Delta S$ does not diverge (stays finite), $T\Delta S\to0$ as $T\to0$, so it is obvious from the equation alone that $\Delta G\to\Delta H$. Before low-temperature research advanced, many researchers took precisely this obvious fact alone to mean that “the behavior as $T\to0$ is understood.”

However, this obvious argument tells us nothing about what specific value $\Delta S$ itself approaches as $T\to0$ (whether it approaches $0$ or stays at some finite value). This is because the fact $\Delta G\to\Delta H$ alone holds equally well whether $\Delta S$ approaches $0$ or approaches some other finite value. What the low-temperature measurements (the measurements that followed Richards's work on electrochemical cells) revealed was a much stronger fact—that when $\Delta G$ is plotted as a function of $T$, the tangent to this curve is horizontal at $T=0$ (the slope $(\partial \Delta G/\partial T)_p$ itself approaches $0$). Since $(\partial \Delta G/\partial T)_p=-\Delta S$, this fact that “the slope approaches $0$” gives precisely the concrete conclusion $\Delta S\to0$, which could not be derived from the earlier obvious argument. It was this nontrivial discovery that later led to Nernst's theorem and the third law of thermodynamics.

Exercise 32.8: $C_p\to0$ and the difficulty of maintaining a low temperature

Solution

The quantity of heat required to raise the temperature from $T_1$ to $T_2$ is $Q=\displaystyle\int_{T_1}^{T_2}bT^3\,\dd T=\dfrac{b}{4}\left(T_2^4-T_1^4\right)$, so

$$ T_2=\left(T_1^4+\frac{4Q}{b}\right)^{1/4} $$

Substituting $Q=1\ \mu\mathrm J=10^{-6}\ \mathrm J$ and $b=4.817\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}K^{-4}}$ gives $4Q/b=4\times10^{-6}/(4.817\times10^{-5})\approx0.0830\ \mathrm{K^4}$.

  • $T_1=1\ \mathrm K$: $T_2=(1+0.0830)^{1/4}\approx1.020\ \mathrm K$. The rise is about $0.02\ \mathrm K$ (almost the same as the approximation $\Delta T=Q/C_p=10^{-6}/(4.817\times10^{-5})\approx0.021\ \mathrm K$).
  • $T_1=0.1\ \mathrm K$: $T_2=(10^{-4}+0.0830)^{1/4}\approx0.537\ \mathrm K$. With the same $1\ \mu\mathrm J$ of heat, a substance at $0.1\ \mathrm K$ is heated to nearly $0.54\ \mathrm K$, so its temperature rises by more than a factor of five (here $\Delta T=Q/C_p$ cannot be used, because $C_p$ changes rapidly with $T$).

The lower the temperature, the more rapidly $C_p=bT^3$ decreases, and the more the temperature rises even for the same quantity of heat. This is one reason why it is difficult to maintain low temperatures near absolute zero.

← Back to Chapter 32, “The Third Law of Thermodynamics and Rules for Phase Transitions”

Chapter 33 The Heat Conduction Equation

Exercise 33.1: Physical meaning of the heat conduction equation

Solution

Recall the derivation of the heat conduction equation in Chapter 33 (the heat balance on a small interval $[x,x+\dd x]$). Suppose the lateral surface of the rod is thermally insulated, so that heat flows only in the $x$ direction. By Fourier's law, the quantity of heat flowing into the interval through cross section A (at position $x$) and the quantity of heat flowing out through cross section B (at position $x+\dd x$) during a short time $\dd t$ are given by

$$ \dd'Q_{\mathrm{A}}=\lambda S\left(-\pdiff Tx\right)_{\!\mathrm{A}}\dd t,\qquad \dd'Q_{\mathrm{B}}=\lambda S\left(-\pdiff Tx\right)_{\!\mathrm{B}}\dd t $$

(both quantities are taken to be positive for heat flow in the $+x$ direction). The net quantity of heat received by the interval is the difference

$$ \dd'Q=\dd'Q_{\mathrm{A}}-\dd'Q_{\mathrm{B}}=\lambda S\left[\left(\pdiff Tx\right)_{\!\mathrm{B}}-\left(\pdiff Tx\right)_{\!\mathrm{A}}\right]\dd t=\lambda S\,\pdiff{^2T}{x^2}\,\dd x\,\dd t $$

Here we used $(\partial T/\partial x)_{\mathrm{B}}-(\partial T/\partial x)_{\mathrm{A}}\approx(\partial^2T/\partial x^2)\dd x$ (Eq. ① of Chapter 33). This quantity of heat raises the temperature of the interval by $\dd T=\dd'Q/(c\rho S\,\dd x)$. That is, the larger $\partial^2T/\partial x^2$ (how sharply the graph of the temperature distribution “bends”; the second derivative) is, the larger the net quantity of heat received by the small interval, and therefore the larger the rate of change of temperature $\partial T/\partial t$ as well.

The meaning of the sign is as follows. At a point where $\partial^2T/\partial x^2\gt0$ (the graph is convex downward, that is, the temperature at that point is lower than the average of the temperatures immediately on either side), the slope $\partial T/\partial x$ is larger on the B side than on the A side. That is, $\dd'Q_{\mathrm{A}}\gt\dd'Q_{\mathrm{B}}$: the inflow exceeds the outflow, so heat accumulates on balance and the temperature rises. Conversely, at a point where $\partial^2T/\partial x^2\lt0$ (the graph is convex upward, a “bump” hotter than its surroundings), the outflow exceeds the inflow and heat escapes to the surroundings, so the temperature falls. The larger the absolute value of $\partial^2T/\partial x^2$, the more extreme this “difference from the surroundings” is (a sharper bump or dip), so the imbalance between the heat entering and leaving is also larger, and the temperature changes faster. This is also the reason why, in the Fourier series solution found in the main text (Theorem 33.2 and Theorem 33.3), the higher-order Fourier components that oscillate finely in space (the components with large $n$, for which the absolute value of the coefficient $-n^2\pi^2/a^2$ corresponding to $\partial^2T/\partial x^2$ is large) decay faster.

(The case $T(x)=T_m\sin(\pi x/a)$) Differentiating twice gives

$$ \pdiff Tx=\frac{\pi}{a}T_m\cos\frac{\pi x}{a},\qquad \pdiff{^2T}{x^2}=-\frac{\pi^2}{a^2}T_m\sin\frac{\pi x}{a} $$

At the midpoint $x=a/2$ we have $\sin(\pi/2)=1$, so $\partial^2T/\partial x^2=-\pi^2T_m/a^2\lt0$ (with $T_m\gt0$), and the graph is convex upward (the top of a hill). Hence, by the heat conduction equation, $\partial T/\partial t=\kappa^2\,\partial^2T/\partial x^2\lt0$, and the temperature at the midpoint falls. This expresses the fact that heat escapes from the top of the hill to both sides, which agrees with the $n=1$ mode of the main text decaying with time.

Exercise 33.2: Why the steady state is a straight line

Solution

The heat conduction equation is $\partial T/\partial t=\kappa^2\,\partial^2T/\partial x^2$. A steady state is a state in which the temperature distribution no longer changes with time, so by definition $\partial T/\partial t=0$. Substituting this into the left-hand side of the heat conduction equation, we obtain

$$ 0=\kappa^2\,\pdiff{^2T}{x^2} $$

Now, $\kappa^2=\lambda/(c\rho)$ is built from positive quantities intrinsic to the material (the thermal conductivity, the specific heat, and the density), so $\kappa^2\neq0$. Therefore we can divide both sides by $\kappa^2$, which yields $\partial^2T/\partial x^2=0$.

In a steady state $T$ does not depend on the time $t$, so we may treat it as a function $T=T(x)$ of $x$ alone, and the partial derivative is the same as an ordinary derivative. Integrating both sides of $\dd^2T/\dd x^2=0$ once with respect to $x$ gives $\dd T/\dd x=A$ (a constant of integration), and integrating once more gives

$$ T(x)=Ax+B $$

(where $A,B$ are constants of integration). This is precisely a linear function of $x$ (a straight line with slope $A$ and intercept $B$). That the second derivative vanishes identically means that “the slope of the graph (the first derivative) is constant,” and the only functions with constant slope are precisely the linear functions (straight lines)—this is why the temperature distribution in a steady state is always a straight line.

Exercise 33.3: Why does a “constant” appear in separation of variables?

Solution

The separated equation was $\dfrac{1}{\kappa^2}\dfrac{\dot\Theta(t)}{\Theta(t)}=\dfrac{X''(x)}{X(x)}$ (the equation in Chapter 33). The left-hand side is an expression in $t$ alone ($x$ does not appear at all), and the right-hand side is an expression in $x$ alone ($t$ does not appear at all).

Now fix $x$ at some single value (say $x=x_1$). Since $x_1$ is fixed, the right-hand side $X''(x_1)/X(x_1)$ is now just a number (a constant independent of $t$). Since the equality holds, the left-hand side $\dfrac{1}{\kappa^2}\dfrac{\dot\Theta(t)}{\Theta(t)}$ must also always equal this same number, however we vary $t$. That is, the left-hand side was in fact a constant independent of $t$.

Next, if we fix $t$ at another value ($t=t_1$) and make the same argument, this time the right-hand side $X''(x)/X(x)$ turns out to be a constant independent of $x$.

Combining the two arguments above, both the left-hand side (an expression in $t$ alone) and the right-hand side (an expression in $x$ alone) equal “one and the same number that depends on neither $x$ nor $t$.” This is the logic by which the “constant $\alpha$” always appears in the method of separation of variables. If the left-hand side really changed its value with $t$, the right-hand side (an expression not containing $t$) could not follow it, contradicting the premise that the identity holds for all $x,t$.

Exercise 33.4: Heat flowing through a copper rod and the time to cool down

Solution

(1) By the formula of Chapter 33 (obtained by dividing $Q=\lambda tS(T_0-T_a)/a$ by $St$), the steady-state heat flux is $q=\lambda\,\Delta T/a$. Substituting $\lambda=403\ \mathrm{W/(m\cdot K)}$, $\Delta T=80\ \mathrm{K}$, and $a=0.10\ \mathrm{m}$, we obtain

$$ q=403\ \mathrm{W/(m\cdot K)}\times\frac{80\ \mathrm{K}}{0.10\ \mathrm{m}}=3.224\times10^{5}\ \mathrm{W/m^2}\approx3.2\times10^{5}\ \mathrm{W/m^2} $$

The cross-sectional area is $S=1.0\ \mathrm{cm^2}=1.0\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{m^2}$, so the rate of heat flow through the rod (the quantity of heat per unit time) is

$$ P=qS=3.224\times10^{5}\ \mathrm{W/m^2}\times1.0\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{m^2}=32.24\ \mathrm{W}\approx32\ \mathrm{W} $$

Even in a thin rod, copper conducts heat so well that a temperature difference of only this size drives as much as about $32\ \mathrm{W}$ of heat flow.

(2) Substituting $a=0.10\ \mathrm{m}$, $\kappa^2=1.17\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{m^2/s}$, and $\pi^2=9.870$ into $\tau_1=a^2/(\pi^2\kappa^2)$, we obtain

$$ \tau_1=\frac{(0.10)^2}{9.870\times1.17\times10^{-4}}=\frac{0.010}{1.155\times10^{-3}}\approx8.7\ \mathrm{s} $$

(the unit is $\mathrm{m^2/(m^2/s)}=\mathrm{s}$). Compared with the rod of Example 33.7 with $a=0.50\ \mathrm{m}$ (about $2.2\times10^{2}\ \mathrm{s}$), the length is now $1/5$ as large, so since $\tau_1\propto a^2$ the time becomes $1/25$ as long, and indeed $217/25\approx8.7$ agrees.

Exercise 33.5: Analogy between the Fourier series and the vibration of a string

Solution

What is the same: Both start from the boundary condition “zero at both ends,” $X(0)=X(a)=0$ (zero displacement at the fixed ends for a string; zero temperature at both ends for heat conduction). Solving the ordinary differential equation for the spatial part, $X''+\omega^2X=0$ (or an equation of the same form), we obtain $X(x)=A\cos\omega x+B\sin\omega x$. The boundary condition $X(0)=0$ gives $A=0$, and $X(a)=0$ gives $\sin\omega a=0$, that is, $\omega a=n\pi$ ($n=1,2,3,\ldots$); exactly the same quantization condition appears in both cases. In other words, the allowed wave shapes in the spatial direction (the eigenfunctions $\sin(n\pi x/a)$) and the allowed wavenumbers $n\pi/a$ are common to the heat conduction equation and the wave equation—this is because they are a purely mathematical consequence determined only by the form of the boundary conditions.

What differs: The ordinary differential equation for the time part $\Theta(t)$ is different. In the heat conduction equation, by separation of variables $\Theta(t)$ obeys the first-order ordinary differential equation $\dot\Theta=\kappa^2\alpha\Theta$ ($\alpha\lt0$), and the solution decays exponentially, $\Theta(t)=e^{-n^2\pi^2\kappa^2t/a^2}$ (it does not oscillate; it approaches 0 monotonically). On the other hand, for the wave equation of the string, $\partial^2u/\partial t^2=v^2\partial^2u/\partial x^2$, we get a second-order ordinary differential equation $\ddot\Theta=v^2\alpha\Theta$ in $t$, and for $\alpha\lt0$ the solution is $\Theta(t)=C_1\cos(n\pi vt/a)+C_2\sin(n\pi vt/a)$, which oscillates in time as well.

The root cause of this difference lies in the equations themselves: the heat conduction equation is first-order in time ($\partial T/\partial t$), whereas the wave equation is second-order in time ($\partial^2u/\partial t^2$). A first-order time derivative gives the equation of decay or growth, “the rate of change is proportional to the present value” (an exponential function), and a second-order time derivative gives the equation of oscillation, “the acceleration is proportional to the displacement” (trigonometric functions)—this has the same structure as the difference in mechanics between the equation for a resistive force proportional to the velocity (first-order, exponential decay) and the equation for the restoring force of a spring (second-order, simple harmonic motion).

Exercise 33.6: Why higher modes decay faster

Solution

The time constant is $\tau_n=a^2/(n^2\pi^2\kappa^2)$. Taking the ratio for $n=1$ and $n=3$, the factors $a^2,\pi^2,\kappa^2$ are common to both cases and cancel, so that

$$ \frac{\tau_1}{\tau_3}=\frac{a^2/(1^2\pi^2\kappa^2)}{a^2/(3^2\pi^2\kappa^2)}=\frac{3^2}{1^2}=9 $$

(Numerically as well, computing with $\kappa^2=1.0\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{m^2/s},\ a=1\ \mathrm{m}$ gives $\tau_1\approx1013\ \mathrm{s}$ and $\tau_3\approx112.6\ \mathrm{s}$, and the ratio agrees with $9.000$.) That is, the $n=3$ mode decays nine times faster than the $n=1$ mode.

What this means is that “fine bumps and dips in a temperature distribution (components with short wavelength, that is, large $n$) disappear much faster than the overall gentle hill (components with long wavelength, that is, small $n$).” Physically, this is because the larger $n$ is, the more rapid the spatial variation is (the shorter the wavelength $2a/n$), and, as we saw in Exercise 33.1, where $\partial^2T/\partial x^2$ (the steepness of the variation) is larger, heat flows in and out more intensely and is evened out faster. As a result, after a sufficiently long time the temperature distribution, no matter how complicated the initial distribution it started from, approaches a smooth shape in which only the lowest-order mode, which decays most slowly ($n=1$, or the steady-state term $T_1(x)$ when the boundary conditions are not 0), survives. This is what we also confirmed numerically in Example 33.6.

Exercise 33.7: First-term approximation of the solution of Case 2

Solution

The solution of Theorem 33.3 is $T(x,t)=T_1(x)+T_2(x,t)$, where the steady-state term is $T_1(x)=T_0+\dfrac{T_a-T_0}{a}x$ and the transient term is $T_2(x,t)=-\displaystyle\sum_{k\text{ odd}}\frac{8Aa^2}{k^3\pi^3}e^{-\kappa^2k^2\pi^2t/a^2}\sin\frac{k\pi x}{a}$.

$t\to\infty$: The transient term approaches 0, so the temperature at the midpoint is given by the steady-state term alone:

$$ T_1\!\left(\frac a2\right)=\frac{T_0+T_a}{2}=\frac{40+20}{2}=30\ {}^\circ\mathrm{C} $$

$t=0$: Substituting $x=0.5\ \mathrm{m}$ into the initial condition $T(x,0)=Ax^2+\beta x+T_0$ (with $\beta=(T_a-T_0)/a-Aa=-20-100=-120\ \mathrm{K/m}$), we obtain

$$ T\!\left(\frac a2,0\right)=100\times0.25-120\times0.5+40=25-60+40=5\ {}^\circ\mathrm{C} $$

(This is also the sum $30-25=5\ {}^\circ\mathrm{C}$ of the steady-state term $30\ {}^\circ\mathrm{C}$ and the transient term $T_2(a/2,0)=-Aa^2/4=-25\ \mathrm{K}$.)

$t=1000\ \mathrm{s}$ (the $k=1$ term only): We have $\dfrac{8Aa^2}{\pi^3}=\dfrac{800}{31.006}=25.80\ \mathrm{K}$, $e^{-\kappa^2\pi^2t/a^2}=e^{-\pi^2\times10^{-4}\times1000}=e^{-0.987}=0.3727$, and $\sin(\pi/2)=1$, so

$$ T_2\!\left(\frac a2,1000\right)\approx-25.80\times0.3727=-9.62\ \mathrm{K} $$

Hence

$$ T\!\left(\frac a2,1000\right)\approx30-9.62=20.4\ {}^\circ\mathrm{C} $$

The $k=3$ term is $+25.80/27\times e^{-8.88}\approx1.3\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{K}$, which is very small and can be neglected. We see that the temperature rises from the initial $5\ {}^\circ\mathrm{C}$ to $20.4\ {}^\circ\mathrm{C}$ after $1000\ \mathrm{s}$ and continues toward the final $30\ {}^\circ\mathrm{C}$ (the midpoint is initially near the bottom of a trough, so heat flows in and its temperature rises; this agrees with the trend of the $t=500\ \mathrm{s}$ curve in Figure 33.5).

Chapter 34 Gateway to Statistical Thermodynamics — Maxwell's Velocity Distribution

Exercise 34.1: What happens if the hypothesis of independence fails

Solution

(a) Direct effect on the pressure formula. The normal force on a collision surface perpendicular to the $x$ axis, as we saw in Section 34.3.2, is $F_x/\dd S=2m\displaystyle\int_{v_x\gt0}v_x^2f\,\dd v_x\dd v_y\dd v_z=m\rho\langle v_x^2\rangle$, and it is determined by the distribution of $v_x$ alone (the marginal distribution obtained by integrating out $v_y,v_z$). Therefore, even if there is a correlation between $v_x$ and $v_y$, that alone does not immediately change the normal pressure on a surface perpendicular to the $x$ axis.

(b) Effect on the derivation of the functional form. If independence does not hold, the distribution function can no longer be written as the product $g(v_x)g(v_y)g(v_z)$, and the derivation of Section 34.2 (the product of the values on the axes, $f=a^{-6}f(v_x,0,0)f(0,v_y,0)f(0,0,v_z)$, and the argument that leads from it to the functional equation $G(x)G(y)=G(x+y)$) cannot be used. Then we cannot fix the functional form of $f$ to be the Gaussian form $Ae^{-\alpha(v_x^2+v_y^2+v_z^2)}$, and neither $p=m\rho/(2\alpha)$ of Section 34.3.2 (the form in which $\alpha$ appears) nor Law 34.1 can be derived. (Note that the form $p=m\rho\langle v_x^2\rangle$ itself holds regardless of the form of $f$. What independence is responsible for is not the isotropy of the pressure but the determination of the functional form of $f$. For example, a non-Gaussian distribution determined by the speed alone has $\langle v_xv_y\rangle=0$ even when the components are not independent, and the pressure can be isotropic.)

(c) The contradiction if a correlation really existed. A correlation of the type “the larger the $v_x$ of a molecule, the larger its $v_y$” means $\langle v_xv_y\rangle\ne0$. This means that momentum in the $y$ direction is carried on net through a surface perpendicular to the $x$ axis (a force parallel to the surface, that is, a shear stress, arises). But a shear force is not observed to appear on the wall of a gas at rest. Moreover, if the width of the distribution differed with direction, as in $\langle v_x^2\rangle\ne\langle v_y^2\rangle$, the normal pressure would differ depending on the orientation of the wall, which contradicts the experimental fact that “the pressure of a gas is the same on a wall of any orientation.”

(d) Effect on the verification in Section 34.5.1. In Section 34.5.1 we used the fact that $\langle v_x^2\rangle=\langle v_y^2\rangle=\langle v_z^2\rangle$ holds automatically as a consequence of treating the three directions on an equal footing, and we compared with the equipartition theorem using $\langle v^2\rangle=3\langle v_x^2\rangle$. If independence and isotropy fail, the equality of the three directions is no longer guaranteed, $\langle v^2\rangle=3\langle v_x^2\rangle$ cannot be used, and the correspondence with the equipartition theorem can no longer be asserted immediately.

Exercise 34.2: The role of the normalization condition

Solution

The normalization condition is the condition that “the total number of molecules in the container equals the integral of the distribution function over the entire range of positions and velocities,” and it appeared as Eq. (34.1) of Chapter 34, $N=V\displaystyle\int f\,\dd v_x\dd v_y\dd v_z$. Dividing both sides by $V$ gives $\rho=N/V=\displaystyle\int f\,\dd v_x\dd v_y\dd v_z$, and substituting $f=Ae^{-\alpha(v_x^2+v_y^2+v_z^2)}$ into this and using the formula for the Gaussian integral gave the relation Eq. (34.6) of Chapter 34, $\rho=A(\pi/\alpha)^{3/2}$.

If we did not impose this normalization condition, $A$ and $\alpha$ could each independently take any positive value. This is because the functional form $f=Ae^{-\alpha(v_x^2+v_y^2+v_z^2)}$ (the form determined from independence and isotropy) always satisfies the requirements that “the velocity components are independent” and “there is no special direction in space,” regardless of the values of $A,\alpha$. Independence and isotropy alone determine the “form” of the function (that it is Gaussian), but not the two constants, its “height” $A$ and its “spread” $\alpha$. The normalization condition brings in the additional information that “the integral of the distribution function over all of space must coincide exactly with the actual number of molecules $N$ (number density $\rho$),” and thereby gives one relation between $A$ and $\alpha$. However, this single relation alone does not determine the two values $A,\alpha$, and one more independent condition was needed (in the text, $p=m\rho/(2\alpha)$, obtained from the pressure calculation, together with the equation of state of an ideal gas; see Section 34.4).

Incidentally, there is another route that does not go through the pressure calculation. From the energy equipartition theorem of Chapter 24 we know that $\langle v_x^2\rangle=k_{\mathrm B}T/m$, while from the Gaussian distribution we can compute $\langle v_x^2\rangle=1/(2\alpha)$ (Section 34.5.1), so setting the two equal determines $\alpha=m/(2k_{\mathrm B}T)$. In this case the equipartition theorem (an already known result) has been used as the “one more condition.”

Exercise 34.3: Comparing the speed of sound and the root-mean-square speed of oxygen molecules

Solution

For oxygen molecules $\mathrm{O_2}$ ($M=32.0\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{kg/mol}$, $\gamma=1.4$), $T=300\ \mathrm K$, and $R=8.314\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)}$, the speed of sound is

$$ v_{\text{sound}}=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma RT}{M}}=\sqrt{\frac{1.4\times8.314\times300}{32.0\times10^{-3}}}\approx330\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

The root-mean-square speed is (Formula 34.3 of Chapter 34)

$$ v_{\mathrm{rms}}=\sqrt{\frac{3RT}{M}}=\sqrt{\frac{3\times8.314\times300}{32.0\times10^{-3}}}\approx484\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

Factoring out the part $RT/M$ common to both expressions, we obtain

$$ \frac{v_{\text{sound}}}{v_{\mathrm{rms}}}=\frac{\sqrt{\gamma RT/M}}{\sqrt{3RT/M}}=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma}{3}} $$

and $R,T,M$ all cancel and disappear—that is, this ratio depends on neither the molar mass $M$ nor the temperature $T$, and is determined by the heat capacity ratio $\gamma$ alone. For a diatomic molecule ($\gamma=1.4$) it is $\sqrt{1.4/3}\approx0.683$, in good agreement with the actual $330/484\approx0.682$ (for a monatomic molecule, $\gamma=5/3$ and $\sqrt{5/9}\approx0.745$). Physically, this result shows that the speed of sound is of roughly the same order of magnitude as the speed of the thermal motion of the molecules (the root-mean-square speed)—since sound is, after all, a phenomenon in which collisions between molecules are passed along, the speed of sound cannot greatly exceed the speed of the thermal motion of the molecules.

Exercise 34.4: Finding $\langle v^4\rangle$

Solution

By the definition of the expectation value, converting to spherical coordinates (as in Section 34.5.1), we have

$$ \langle v^4\rangle=\left(\frac{m}{2\pi k_{\mathrm B}T}\right)^{3/2}\cdot4\pi\int_0^{\infty}v^4\cdot v^2e^{-\frac{mv^2}{2k_{\mathrm B}T}}\,\dd v =\left(\frac{m}{2\pi k_{\mathrm B}T}\right)^{3/2}\cdot4\pi\int_0^{\infty}v^6e^{-\frac{mv^2}{2k_{\mathrm B}T}}\,\dd v $$

Now let $x=\dfrac{mv^2}{2k_{\mathrm B}T}$. Then $v^2=\dfrac{2k_{\mathrm B}T}{m}x$ and $\dd x=\dfrac{mv}{k_{\mathrm B}T}\dd v$, so $v\,\dd v=\dfrac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{m}\dd x$. Grouping $v^6\dd v=v^5\cdot(v\,\dd v)$ and using $v^5=(v^2)^{5/2}=\left(\dfrac{2k_{\mathrm B}T}{m}x\right)^{5/2}$, we get

$$ \int_0^{\infty}v^6e^{-\frac{mv^2}{2k_{\mathrm B}T}}\,\dd v =\left(\frac{2k_{\mathrm B}T}{m}\right)^{5/2}\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{m}\int_0^{\infty}x^{5/2}e^{-x}\,\dd x =4\sqrt2\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{m}\right)^{7/2}\Gamma\!\left(\frac72\right) $$

(We used $2^{5/2}=4\sqrt2$.) Here $\Gamma(7/2)=\dfrac52\cdot\dfrac32\cdot\dfrac12\Gamma\!\left(\dfrac12\right)=\dfrac{15}{8}\sqrt\pi$, so

$$ \int_0^{\infty}v^6e^{-\frac{mv^2}{2k_{\mathrm B}T}}\,\dd v=4\sqrt2\cdot\frac{15}{8}\sqrt\pi\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{m}\right)^{7/2}=\frac{15\sqrt{2\pi}}{2}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{m}\right)^{7/2} $$

Substituting this and using $\left(\dfrac{m}{2\pi k_{\mathrm B}T}\right)^{3/2}\left(\dfrac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{m}\right)^{7/2}=(2\pi)^{-3/2}\left(\dfrac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{m}\right)^2$, we obtain

$$ \langle v^4\rangle=4\pi\cdot\frac{15\sqrt{2\pi}}{2}\cdot(2\pi)^{-3/2}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{m}\right)^2 =\frac{4\pi\cdot15}{2}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{m}\right)^2 =30\pi\cdot\frac1{2\pi}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{m}\right)^2=15\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{m}\right)^2 $$

(We used $\sqrt{2\pi}/(2\pi)^{3/2}=1/(2\pi)$.) This shows that $\langle v^4\rangle=15(k_{\mathrm B}T/m)^2$. The dimension is $(\mathrm{J/kg})^2=(\mathrm{m^2/s^2})^2=\mathrm{m^4/s^4}$, which agrees with that of $v^4$. Also, since $\langle v^2\rangle=3k_{\mathrm B}T/m$,

$$ \frac{\langle v^4\rangle}{\langle v^2\rangle^2}=\frac{15}{9}=\frac53\approx1.67 $$

If all the molecules had the same speed $v_0$, we would have $\langle v^4\rangle/\langle v^2\rangle^2=1$, so $5/3$ is greater than 1. The more this ratio deviates from 1, the broader the distribution of speeds is (the longer the tail of fast molecules).

Exercise 34.5: Dependence of the most probable speed on temperature and mass

Solution

(a) Since $v_{\mathrm p}=\sqrt{2RT/M}$ is proportional to $M^{-1/2}$ at the same temperature,

$$ \frac{v_{\mathrm p}(\mathrm{H_2})}{v_{\mathrm p}(\mathrm{N_2})}=\sqrt{\frac{M_{\mathrm{N_2}}}{M_{\mathrm{H_2}}}}=\sqrt{\frac{28.0}{2.02}}\approx3.72 $$

The most probable speed of $\mathrm{H_2}$ is

$$ v_{\mathrm p}(\mathrm{H_2})=\sqrt{\frac{2\times8.314\times300}{2.02\times10^{-3}}}\approx1.57\times10^3\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

(This agrees with 3.72 times the value $v_{\mathrm p}(\mathrm{N_2})\approx422\ \mathrm{m/s}$ of Example 34.6: $422\times3.72\approx1570$.)

(b) Since $v_{\mathrm p}\propto\sqrt T$, to double $v_{\mathrm p}$ it suffices to double $\sqrt T$, that is, to multiply $T$ by $2^2=4$. We get $T=4\times300\ \mathrm K=1200\ \mathrm K$.

Exercise 34.6: The average of one velocity component

Solution

(a) We have $\langle v_x\rangle=\sqrt{\alpha/\pi}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}v_xe^{-\alpha v_x^2}\dd v_x$, and the integrand $v_xe^{-\alpha v_x^2}$ is an odd function (it changes sign under $v_x\to-v_x$), so the positive and negative contributions cancel and $\langle v_x\rangle=0$. This corresponds to the fact that the gas as a whole is at rest, and there are equal numbers of molecules flying in the $+x$ direction and in the $-x$ direction.

(b) Since the integrand is even, the mean of $|v_x|$ is twice the one-sided value:

$$ \langle|v_x|\rangle=2\sqrt{\frac\alpha\pi}\int_0^{\infty}v_xe^{-\alpha v_x^2}\dd v_x=2\sqrt{\frac\alpha\pi}\cdot\frac1{2\alpha}=\frac1{\sqrt{\pi\alpha}}=\sqrt{\frac{2k_{\mathrm B}T}{\pi m}} $$

(We used $\int_0^\infty xe^{-\alpha x^2}\dd x=\big[-e^{-\alpha x^2}/(2\alpha)\big]_0^\infty=1/(2\alpha)$ and $\alpha=m/(2k_{\mathrm B}T)$.) On the other hand, since $\langle v_x^2\rangle=k_{\mathrm B}T/m$ (Eq. (34.12) of Chapter 34), the standard deviation is $\sqrt{\langle v_x^2\rangle}=\sqrt{k_{\mathrm B}T/m}$, and the ratio is

$$ \frac{\langle|v_x|\rangle}{\sqrt{\langle v_x^2\rangle}}=\sqrt{\frac2\pi}\approx0.798 $$

The “mean of the absolute value” is smaller than the “square root of the mean square.”

(c) For nitrogen ($M=28.0\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{kg/mol}$) at $T=300\ \mathrm K$, using $k_{\mathrm B}/m=R/M$ we have

$$ \langle|v_x|\rangle=\sqrt{\frac{2RT}{\pi M}}=\sqrt{\frac{2\times8.314\times300}{\pi\times28.0\times10^{-3}}}\approx238\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

(The standard deviation is $\sqrt{RT/M}\approx298\ \mathrm{m/s}$.) Also, the ratio of $\langle v\rangle=\sqrt{8k_{\mathrm B}T/(\pi m)}$ (Formula 34.2) to the mean absolute value of the velocity component found above is

$$ \frac{\langle v\rangle}{\langle|v_x|\rangle}=\frac{\sqrt{8/\pi}}{\sqrt{2/\pi}}=\sqrt4=2 $$

and numerically, too, $476/238=2$ agrees.

Exercise 34.7: Why Earth's atmosphere is poor in hydrogen and helium

Solution

(a) Computing $v_{\mathrm{rms}}=\sqrt{3RT/M}$ ($T=300\ \mathrm K$, $R=8.314\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)}$), we obtain

$$ v_{\mathrm{rms}}(\mathrm{H_2})=\sqrt{\frac{3\times8.314\times300}{2.02\times10^{-3}}}\approx1.92\ \mathrm{km/s},\quad v_{\mathrm{rms}}(\mathrm{He})\approx1.37\ \mathrm{km/s},\quad v_{\mathrm{rms}}(\mathrm{N_2})\approx0.517\ \mathrm{km/s} $$

The ratios to $v_{\mathrm{esc}}=11.2\ \mathrm{km/s}$ are

$$ \frac{v_{\mathrm{esc}}}{v_{\mathrm{rms}}}\approx5.8\ (\mathrm{H_2}),\quad8.2\ (\mathrm{He}),\quad21.7\ (\mathrm{N_2}) $$

The lighter the molecule, the closer $v_{\mathrm{rms}}$ is to the escape velocity.

(b) From Example 34.7, with $u_0=v_{\mathrm{esc}}/v_{\mathrm p}$ ($v_{\mathrm p}=\sqrt{2RT/M}$), the fraction is $\dfrac{2}{\sqrt\pi}u_0e^{-u_0^2}+\operatorname{erfc}(u_0)$. Since $v_{\mathrm p}$ is $1.571,\ 1.117,\ 0.422\ \mathrm{km/s}$ for $\mathrm{H_2}$, $\mathrm{He}$, and $\mathrm{N_2}$, respectively, we have $u_0\approx7.13,\ 10.03,\ 26.5$. When $u_0$ is large, the first term dominates, and the common logarithm can be estimated as $-u_0^2/\ln10+\log_{10}(2u_0/\sqrt\pi)$. For example, for $\mathrm{H_2}$ we have $u_0^2\approx50.8$, so $-50.8/2.303\approx-22.06$, and $\log_{10}(2\times7.13/1.772)\approx\log_{10}8.04\approx0.91$; together these give about $-21.15$, that is, the fraction is about $7\times10^{-22}$. Similarly, it is about $2\times10^{-43}$ for $\mathrm{He}$ and about $5\times10^{-305}$ for $\mathrm{N_2}$.

(c) If the temperature stays at $300\ \mathrm K$, for every gas the fraction of molecules exceeding the escape velocity is extremely small, but the degree of smallness differs by many orders of magnitude. Compared with about $10^{-21}$ for $\mathrm{H_2}$, $\mathrm{N_2}$ is effectively zero (around $10^{-304}$). Since the fraction has the form $e^{-u_0^2}$, that is, $e^{-Mv_{\mathrm{esc}}^2/(2RT)}$, it is exponentially sensitive to the mass $M$, so the lighter the molecule, the vastly greater the number of fast molecules exceeding the escape velocity. In the actual upper atmosphere the temperature is much higher (the larger $T$ is, the smaller $u_0$ becomes), and in addition many billions of years have passed, so this small fraction accumulates, and the lighter the gas, the more easily it escapes into space.

Exercise 34.8: Effusion of gas through a small hole

Solution

(a) Using the same reasoning as in Section 34.3.2, the number of molecules with velocities in the range $(v_x,v_y,v_z)\sim(v_x+\dd v_x,\ldots)$ that collide with a wall of area $\dd S$ during $\Delta t$ is the number of molecules in a column of base area $\dd S$ and height $v_x\Delta t$, namely $v_x\Delta t\,\dd S\,f\,\dd v_x\dd v_y\dd v_z$ ($v_x\gt0$). Dividing this by $\Delta t\,\dd S$ and summing over the entire range of velocities, the number of collisions per unit time and unit area, $\Phi$, is

$$ \Phi=\int_0^{\infty}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}v_xf\,\dd v_x\dd v_y\dd v_z =\rho\left(\frac\alpha\pi\right)^{3/2}\cdot\frac{1}{2\alpha}\cdot\frac\pi\alpha=\rho\,\frac{\alpha^{3/2}}{\pi^{3/2}}\cdot\frac{\pi}{2\alpha^2}=\frac{\rho}{2\sqrt{\pi\alpha}} $$

(we used $f=\rho(\alpha/\pi)^{3/2}e^{-\alpha v^2}$ and $\int_0^\infty v_xe^{-\alpha v_x^2}\dd v_x=1/(2\alpha)$, and, for the integrals over $v_y,v_z$, $\sqrt{\pi/\alpha}$ for each). Since $\alpha=m/(2k_{\mathrm B}T)$ gives $\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\alpha}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{2k_{\mathrm B}T}{\pi m}}$, we have

$$ \Phi=\frac\rho2\sqrt{\frac{2k_{\mathrm B}T}{\pi m}}=\rho\sqrt{\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{2\pi m}} $$

On the other hand, $\dfrac14\langle v\rangle=\dfrac14\sqrt{\dfrac{8k_{\mathrm B}T}{\pi m}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{8k_{\mathrm B}T}{16\pi m}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{2\pi m}}$, so $\Phi=\tfrac14\rho\langle v\rangle$ has been shown.

(b) From Example 34.4, $\rho=p/(k_{\mathrm B}T)=2.41\times10^{25}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$, and from Example 34.6, $\langle v\rangle\approx476\ \mathrm{m/s}$. Since $S=1.0\ \mathrm{mm^2}=1.0\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{m^2}$, the number of molecules leaving per second is

$$ \Phi S=\frac14\times2.41\times10^{25}\times476\times1.0\times10^{-6}\approx2.9\times10^{21}\ \text{molecules/s} $$

(In terms of the amount of substance, this is $2.9\times10^{21}/6.02\times10^{23}\approx4.8\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{mol/s}$.)

(c) For the same $p,T$, $\rho=p/(k_{\mathrm B}T)$ is the same regardless of the kind of gas, so the ratio of the numbers of molecules effusing equals the ratio of $\langle v\rangle$, and since $\langle v\rangle\propto M^{-1/2}$,

$$ \frac{\Phi_{\mathrm{He}}}{\Phi_{\mathrm{N_2}}}=\sqrt{\frac{M_{\mathrm{N_2}}}{M_{\mathrm{He}}}}=\sqrt{\frac{28.0}{4.00}}=\sqrt7\approx2.65 $$

This result—that the rate of effusion of a gas through a small hole is inversely proportional to the square root of the molar mass—is Graham's law (the law of effusion).

← Back to Chapter 34, “Gateway to Statistical Thermodynamics — Maxwell's Velocity Distribution”

Chapter 35 Coulomb's Law and Gauss's Law

Exercise 35.1: Flux from a point charge placed at a vertex of a cube

Solution

Using a large cube. If we attach eight cubes (side $a$), each having the point charge $Q$ at a vertex, around the charge, we obtain a large cube of side $2a$ centered on the charge. The surface of the large cube is a closed surface, and the only charge inside is $Q$, so by Gauss's law (integral form) the flux through the entire surface is

$$ \iint_{\text{large cube}}\bm E\cdot\bm n\,\dd S=\frac{Q}{\varepsilon_0} $$

The large cube has a symmetry centered on the charge (interchanges of faces, rotations, and reflections), so the fluxes through the six faces are all equal, each being $\dfrac{Q}{6\varepsilon_0}$. Furthermore, one face (a square of side $2a$) is divided by two lines through the midpoints of its edges into four small squares of side $a$. The four small squares are interchanged with one another by reflections in the two planes that pass through the center of the face and are parallel to its edges, so the flux through each of them is also the same, namely

$$ \frac{1}{4}\times\frac{Q}{6\varepsilon_0}=\frac{Q}{24\varepsilon_0} $$

per small square (side $a$).

(a) The surface of the large cube consists of 24 such small squares ($=6\times4$). The three faces of the original cube (side $a$, with the charge at one vertex) that do not contain the charge coincide exactly with three of the small squares on the surface of the large cube. Therefore, the flux through one of these faces is $\dfrac{Q}{24\varepsilon_0}$. Numerically,

$$ \frac{Q}{24\varepsilon_0}=\frac{3.0\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm C}{24\times8.854\times10^{-12}\ \mathrm{C^2/(N\,m^2)}}\approx14.1\ \mathrm{N\,m^2/C} $$

(the unit is $\mathrm{N\,m^2/C}=\mathrm{V\,m}$). The three faces have a symmetry under which they are interchanged with one another around the three edges passing through a vertex of the cube, so the value is the same for each face.

(b) The three faces that contain the charge lie in planes passing through the charge (the vertex). A line drawn from the charge to a point on such a face lies within the face, so the electric field of the point charge, $\bm E\parallel\bm r$, is parallel to the face everywhere on it. The normal vector $\bm n$ of the face is perpendicular to the face, so $\bm E\cdot\bm n=0$, and the flux is $0$.

(c) The flux through the entire surface of the cube is the sum of the three faces in (a) and the three faces in (b):

$$ 3\times\frac{Q}{24\varepsilon_0}+3\times0=\frac{Q}{8\varepsilon_0}\approx42.4\ \mathrm{N\,m^2/C} $$

This also agrees with the self-evident result that each of the eight cubes accounts for $\dfrac18$ of the total flux $Q/\varepsilon_0$. The side length $a$ does not appear in the result. Therefore, the flux does not change even if $a$ is doubled. This is due to a property of Gauss's law: the electric field of a point charge weakens in proportion to $1/r^2$, while the area of a surface grows in proportion to $r^2$, so the two effects cancel (the flux through a surface is determined only by the solid angle that the surface subtends as seen from the charge).

Exercise 35.2: Basic exercise on divergence and gradient

Solution

The divergence of $\bm F=(x^2y,\ y^2z,\ z^2x)$ is obtained by taking the partial derivative of each component with respect to its own variable and adding the results (as in the definitions in Section 35.3 and in University Mathematics, Section 19.1). We have $\pdiff{}{x}(x^2y)=2xy$ ($y$ is treated as a constant), $\pdiff{}{y}(y^2z)=2yz$ ($z$ is treated as a constant), and $\pdiff{}{z}(z^2x)=2zx$ ($x$ is treated as a constant), so

$$ \operatorname{div}\bm F=2xy+2yz+2zx $$

The gradient of $\varphi=x^2+y^2+z^2$ is the vector formed by arranging the three partial derivatives with respect to each variable, so from $\pdiff\varphi x=2x,\ \pdiff\varphi y=2y,\ \pdiff\varphi z=2z$ we get

$$ \grad\varphi=(2x,\ 2y,\ 2z) $$

(Since $\varphi=\abs{\bm r}^2$, this can also be written $\grad\varphi=2\bm r$.)

Next, for the position vector field $\bm f=(x,y,z)$ (its divergence, computed according to Definition 19.2 as the three-dimensional version of Illustration 35.1, is $\operatorname{div}\bm f=\pdiff xx+\pdiff yy+\pdiff zz=1+1+1=3$), we compute directly the surface integral over the entire surface of a sphere of radius $R$. At each point on the sphere, $\bm f=(x,y,z)$ is the position vector of that point itself; its magnitude equals the radius $R$ of the sphere, and its direction is outward from the center of the sphere—that is, exactly the same direction as the outward normal $\bm n$ of the sphere. Therefore, at every point on the sphere $\bm f\cdot\bm n=\abs{\bm f}\abs{\bm n}\cos0°=R\times1=R$ (constant), and since the surface area of the sphere is $4\pi R^2$,

$$ \iint_S\bm f\cdot\bm n\,\dd S=R\times4\pi R^2=4\pi R^3 $$

On the other hand, the right-hand side of the divergence theorem is the product of the divergence $\operatorname{div}\bm f=3$ (constant) and the volume of the sphere, $\frac43\pi R^3$, so

$$ \iiint_V(\operatorname{div}\bm f)\,\dd V=3\times\frac43\pi R^3=4\pi R^3 $$

Thus the surface integral (left-hand side) and the volume integral (right-hand side) both equal $4\pi R^3$ and agree—we have confirmed that Gauss's divergence theorem (University Mathematics, Section 20.5) also holds in this concrete example.

Exercise 35.3: Correcting a missing $\frac12$

Solution

The equation of motion is $m\ddot x=qE$. The right-hand side $qE$ is constant, so the acceleration $\ddot x=qE/m\equiv a$ is also constant. Integrating this once with respect to time (with constant of integration $C_1$), we get

$$ \dot x(t)=at+C_1 $$

Substituting the initial condition $\dot x(0)=v_0$ gives $\dot x(0)=C_1=v_0$, so $\dot x(t)=at+v_0$. Integrating once more with respect to time (with constant of integration $C_2$), we get

$$ x(t)=\frac12at^2+v_0t+C_2 $$

Substituting the initial condition $x(0)=x_0$ gives $x(0)=C_2=x_0$, so the correct expression is

$$ x(t)=x_0+v_0t+\frac12at^2=x_0+v_0t+\frac12\cdot\frac{qE}{m}\,t^2 $$

The incorrect expression $x(t)=x_0+v_0t+\dfrac{qE}{m}t^2$ lacks the coefficient $\dfrac12$; the second of the two integrations (when the velocity expression $\dot x=at+v_0$ is integrated once more, the term $\frac12at^2$ arises from the $at$ term) has not been carried out correctly. (This is the same material as in Sections 1.4 and 35.6. Note that differentiating the correct expression twice with respect to $t$ gives back $\ddot x=a=qE/m$, in agreement with the equation of motion. The incorrect expression gives $\ddot x=2qE/m$, which does not match.)

Exercise 35.4: The force between two point charges of 1 coulomb

Solution

Substituting $q=Q=1\ \mathrm C$ and $r=1\ \mathrm m$ into Coulomb's law (Law 35.1 of Chapter 35), we obtain

$$ \abs{\bm F}=\frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\cdot\frac{(1\ \mathrm C)(1\ \mathrm C)}{(1\ \mathrm m)^2}=k\times1\approx8.988\times10^{9}\ \mathrm N $$

This is an enormous force of about 9 billion newtons. The mass $M$ of an object whose weight $Mg$ equals this force is, from $F=Mg$,

$$ M=\frac{F}{g}=\frac{8.988\times10^{9}\ \mathrm N}{9.8\ \mathrm{m/s^2}}\approx9.2\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{kg} $$

This is the same magnitude as the gravitational force acting on an object of about 920,000 metric tons (the weight of about three very large crude-oil tankers of the 300,000-deadweight-ton class). The fact that a charge of just $1\ \mathrm C$, an amount that does not feel especially large in everyday life, produces such an enormous force is consistent with the conclusion “the Coulomb force is far stronger than universal gravitation” seen in Example 35.1 of Section 35.1—in practice, gathering as much as $1\ \mathrm C$ of charge at a single point is itself extremely difficult, because of the strong repulsion between charges of the same sign.

Exercise 35.5: Electric field lines around a positive and a negative point charge

Solution

The electric field lines start radially from the positive charge $+q_0$, curve along the direction of the electric field on the way, and enter the negative charge $-q_0$. On the line segment joining the two charges, the electric field line is a straight line going from $+q_0$ to $-q_0$, and on both sides of it, the electric field lines leaving $+q_0$ draw arcs and come back to $-q_0$. Some electric field lines also head to places far from the two charges; these curve around the charge on the opposite side while gradually spreading out radially (the general shape is shown in the figure below; it is the pattern of electric field lines of an electric dipole, the same shape as that of the magnetic field lines that emerge from the N pole of a magnet and return to the S pole).

Next, let us examine the electric field on the perpendicular bisecting plane of the segment joining the two charges (the set of points equidistant from the two charges). Let the separation of the two charges be $2a$, with $+q_0$ placed at $(-a,0)$ and $-q_0$ at $(a,0)$. At a point $\mathrm P=(0,s)$ on the bisecting plane (at distance $s$ from the midpoint), the distance from each charge is the same, $\sqrt{a^2+s^2}$, so the two electric fields have equal magnitude, $E_1=\dfrac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\dfrac{q_0}{a^2+s^2}$. As for direction, the field of $+q_0$ points from $+q_0$ toward $\mathrm P$, and the field of $-q_0$ points from $\mathrm P$ toward $-q_0$, so in components,

$$ \bm E_+=E_1\cdot\frac{(a,\ s)}{\sqrt{a^2+s^2}},\qquad \bm E_-=E_1\cdot\frac{(a,\ -s)}{\sqrt{a^2+s^2}} $$

The components of the two fields perpendicular to the direction joining the two charges (the $y$ components, i.e., the components parallel to the bisecting plane) are $\pm E_1s/\sqrt{a^2+s^2}$, which are opposite in direction and equal in magnitude, so they cancel each other. On the other hand, the components along the direction joining the two charges (the $x$ components) are both $+E_1a/\sqrt{a^2+s^2}$, and they reinforce each other in the same direction (from the $+q_0$ side to the $-q_0$ side). Therefore,

$$ \bm E=\bm E_++\bm E_-=\left(\frac{2E_1a}{\sqrt{a^2+s^2}},\ 0\right)=\left(\frac{q_0\,a}{2\pi\varepsilon_0\,(a^2+s^2)^{3/2}},\ 0\right) $$

Thus, on the perpendicular bisecting plane the electric field is perpendicular to the bisecting plane (parallel to the line joining the two charges, pointing from the $+q_0$ side to the $-q_0$ side), and the electric field lines cross this plane at right angles. (For example, with $q_0=1\ \mathrm{nC}$, $a=1\ \mathrm m$, and $s=1.5\ \mathrm m$, the components of the field of each charge are $(1.53,\ \pm2.30)\ \mathrm{V/m}$ and the total is $(3.07,\ 0)\ \mathrm{V/m}$, which confirms numerically that the $y$ components cancel.)

Exercise 35.6: Explaining the equivalence of the integral and differential forms in your own words

Solution

The integral form of Gauss's law $\displaystyle\iint_S\bm E\cdot\bm n\,\dd S=Q_{\text{enc}}/\varepsilon_0$ is the statement that “for a closed surface $S$ of any shape, the net flux of the electric field through $S$ is determined by the total amount of charge inside $S$.” The differential form $\operatorname{div}\bm E=\rho/\varepsilon_0$, on the other hand, is a local statement (one that focuses on a single point only): “at each point in space, the rate at which the electric field emanates from the immediate neighborhood of that point (per unit volume) is determined by the charge density at that point”—at first glance, one is a statement about information on “the whole surface” and the other about information at “a single point,” so the scales are completely different.

What connects the two is Gauss's divergence theorem $\displaystyle\iint_S\bm f\cdot\bm n\,\dd S=\iiint_V(\operatorname{div}\bm f)\,\dd V$ (University Mathematics, Section 20.5). The content of this theorem rests on a “cancellation” argument (Section 35.3 and the sketch of the proof in University Mathematics, Section 20.5): “If the region $V$ is divided into countless tiny volumes, then on an internal face shared by adjacent tiny volumes, the outward flux as seen from one volume and the outward flux as seen from its neighbor have exactly opposite signs and equal magnitudes (since we are merely viewing the same face from opposite sides), so when everything is added up, all the internal faces cancel in pairs and vanish. What survives is only the part on the outer boundary surface $S$, which is not shared with any tiny volume.”

Applying this theorem to the left-hand side of the integral form gives $\displaystyle\iiint_V(\operatorname{div}\bm E)\,\dd V=Q_{\text{enc}}/\varepsilon_0=\dfrac1{\varepsilon_0}\displaystyle\iiint_V\rho\,\dd V$, an equality between volume integrals. This equality holds no matter how small $V$ is made and wherever it is taken. In particular, if we shrink $V$ indefinitely around a single point in space, the value of the volume integral approaches (provided the integrand is continuous at that point) the value of the integrand at that point multiplied by the volume. Dividing both sides by that tiny volume and taking the limit, the volume-integral sign drops out, and we obtain the differential-form equation $\operatorname{div}\bm E=\rho/\varepsilon_0$, which concerns a single point only. Conversely, if the differential form holds (at every point in the region), then integrating it over the whole volume $V$ and using the divergence theorem from right to left (that is, using the same equality in the reverse direction), we obtain the integral form. Thus the relation is this: “the sum over all of space of the relations for tiny volumes is the integral form, and the integral form collapsed to a single point is the differential form.” Because the divergence theorem ties this “adding up” operation and this “collapsing to a point” operation together with neither excess nor deficiency, the two forms are mathematically completely equivalent.

← Back to Chapter 35, “Coulomb's Law and Gauss's Law”

Chapter 36 Electric Potential and Capacitors

Exercise 36.1: Deriving the potential of a point charge from Coulomb's law

Solution

By Coulomb's law, the electric field around a point charge $Q$ placed at the origin points in the radial direction and has magnitude

$$ E(r)=\frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\frac{Q}{r^2} $$

By the definition of potential difference (Section 36.1 of the main text), the electric potential at a point $A$ at distance $r$ from the origin, taking infinity as the reference ($\varphi_\infty=0$), is $\varphi(r)=-\displaystyle\int_\infty^A\bm E\cdot\dd\bm r$. If we choose as the integration path a straight line from infinity to $A$ (in the radial direction), the electric field and the direction of the path are aligned, so the inner product (dot product) becomes a mere product. Writing the distance of a point on the path from the origin as the integration variable $r'$ (so that $r'=r$ at the point $A$), we have

$$ \varphi(r)=-\int_{r'=\infty}^{r'=r}E(r')\,\dd r'=-\int_\infty^r\frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\frac{Q}{r'^2}\,\dd r' $$

Using the fact that an antiderivative of $1/r'^2$ is $-1/r'$ (one need only check that differentiating it returns $\dd(-1/r')/\dd r'=1/r'^2$), we obtain

$$ \varphi(r)=-\frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\left[-\frac{Q}{r'}\right]_\infty^r=-\frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\left(-\frac{Q}{r}-\lim_{c\to\infty}\left(-\frac{Q}{c}\right)\right) $$

The limit inside the parentheses is $\lim_{c\to\infty}(-Q/c)=0$ (the denominator diverges to infinity, so the fraction as a whole approaches 0), and therefore

$$ \varphi(r)=-\frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\left(-\frac{Q}{r}\right)=\frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\frac{Q}{r} $$

which agrees with Formula 36.2 of the main text.

Exercise 36.2: Two infinite plane charges of opposite signs

Solution

Viewed individually, a plane of surface density $+\sigma$ and a plane of surface density $-\sigma$ each produce an electric field of magnitude $\sigma/(2\varepsilon_0)$ (Example 36.4 of the main text, the electric field of an infinite plane, $E=\sigma/(2\varepsilon_0)$). The field points outward on both sides of the $+\sigma$ plane (away from it), and inward from both sides toward the $-\sigma$ plane (toward it). Now place the $+\sigma$ plane on the left and the $-\sigma$ plane on the right, and take rightward as positive.

(1) The region between the two planes: This region is on the right side of the left $+\sigma$ plane (between the two planes), so the field produced by $+\sigma$ points rightward, with magnitude $\sigma/(2\varepsilon_0)$. It is on the left side of the right $-\sigma$ plane (again between the two planes), so the field produced by $-\sigma$ also points rightward (toward $-\sigma$), with magnitude $\sigma/(2\varepsilon_0)$. Both fields point in the same (rightward) direction, so they add:

$$ E_{\text{between}}=\frac{\sigma}{2\varepsilon_0}+\frac{\sigma}{2\varepsilon_0}=\frac{\sigma}{\varepsilon_0}\quad(\text{rightward, pointing from + to −}) $$

(2) The regions outside the two planes: For example, on the left (farther left than the $+\sigma$ plane), the field produced by $+\sigma$ points leftward (outward), with magnitude $\sigma/(2\varepsilon_0)$. On the other hand, the field produced by $-\sigma$ points, as seen from there, toward $-\sigma$, that is, rightward, with magnitude $\sigma/(2\varepsilon_0)$. The magnitudes are equal and the directions opposite, so they cancel:

$$ E_{\text{outside}}=\frac{\sigma}{2\varepsilon_0}-\frac{\sigma}{2\varepsilon_0}=0 $$

For the outer region on the right, the same argument with left and right interchanged gives $0$ as well. In summary, between two parallel plane charges of opposite sign there is a uniform electric field $\sigma/\varepsilon_0$ (directed from + to −), and outside them there is no electric field—this is precisely the superposition argument for the electric field of a parallel-plate capacitor used in Section 36.6 of the main text.

Exercise 36.3: Equivalent capacitance of capacitors connected in series and in parallel

Solution

(1) Series connection: When two capacitors $C_1,C_2$ are connected in a single line, the two plates joined by the wire in between (the negative plate of $C_1$ and the positive plate of $C_2$) can be regarded as a single conductor isolated from the outside. Since this isolated conductor initially carried charge $0$, the sum of the charges produced on it by electrostatic induction must always be $0$. Therefore, if $+Q$ is placed on $C_1$, then $-Q$ is induced on the connected conductor, and the positive plate of $C_2$, which pairs with it, receives $+Q$—that is, the capacitors $C_1,C_2$ carry a common charge $Q$. The total potential difference is the sum of the potential differences of the two capacitors (the potential difference of the battery is split exactly into the two potential differences along the way):

$$ V=V_1+V_2=\frac{Q}{C_1}+\frac{Q}{C_2}=Q\left(\frac1{C_1}+\frac1{C_2}\right) $$

From the definition of the equivalent capacitance $C=Q/V$ (the same form as Definition 36.4 of the main text),

$$ \frac1C=\frac VQ=\frac1{C_1}+\frac1{C_2} $$

(2) Parallel connection: When two capacitors are connected in parallel, both are connected directly to the same two wires (the + terminal and the − terminal of the battery), so the potential difference $V$ is common to both. The total charge is the sum of the charges stored in the two capacitors:

$$ Q=Q_1+Q_2=C_1V+C_2V=(C_1+C_2)V $$

Therefore, the equivalent capacitance is

$$ C=\frac QV=C_1+C_2 $$

Correspondence with resistors: The rules for combining resistances, which we will learn in Section 37.5, are $R=R_1+R_2$ for a series connection (a simple sum) and $1/R=1/R_1+1/R_2$ for a parallel connection (the reciprocal of the sum of the reciprocals). For capacitors it is exactly the opposite: for a series connection we take the reciprocal of the sum of the reciprocals, and for a parallel connection we take a simple sum. This is because the quantity that is common has been interchanged. In a series connection of resistors, the current $I$ is common and the voltage is divided. In a series connection of capacitors, the charge $Q$ is common and the voltage is divided ($V_1:V_2=\dfrac1{C_1}:\dfrac1{C_2}$). In a parallel connection, the voltage $V$ is common in both cases. The difference in the common quantity is the reason why the forms of the formulas (sum and reciprocal sum) are interchanged.

Exercise 36.4: A spherical charge distribution whose density decreases linearly from the center to the surface

Solution

We carry out the same procedure as in Examples 36.7 and 36.8 of the main text, now with $\rho=\rho_0(1-r/a)$.

Electric field: Taking a sphere of radius $r$ as the Gaussian surface, for $0\le r\le a$ the enclosed charge is (using the integral of the angular part, $\displaystyle\int_0^{2\pi}\!\!\int_0^\pi\sin\theta\,\dd\theta\,\dd\phi=4\pi$)

$$ Q(r)=4\pi\int_0^r\rho_0\left(1-\frac{r'}{a}\right)r'^2\,\dd r'=4\pi\rho_0\left[\frac{r'^3}{3}-\frac{r'^4}{4a}\right]_0^r=4\pi\rho_0\left(\frac{r^3}{3}-\frac{r^4}{4a}\right) $$

From Gauss's law $E(r)\cdot4\pi r^2=Q(r)/\varepsilon_0$,

$$ E(r)=\frac{Q(r)}{4\pi\varepsilon_0r^2}=\frac{\rho_0}{\varepsilon_0}\left(\frac r3-\frac{r^2}{4a}\right)\qquad(0\le r\le a) $$

The total charge is $Q=Q(a)=4\pi\rho_0a^3\left(\dfrac13-\dfrac14\right)=\dfrac{\pi}{3}\rho_0a^3$. For $r\gt a$, the total charge inside is $Q$, so

$$ E(r)=\frac{Q}{4\pi\varepsilon_0r^2}=\frac{\rho_0a^3}{12\varepsilon_0}\cdot\frac1{r^2}\qquad(r\gt a) $$

Position of the maximum electric field: Differentiating the interior expression with respect to $r$ and setting the result equal to $0$, we obtain $\dfrac{\dd E}{\dd r}=\dfrac{\rho_0}{\varepsilon_0}\left(\dfrac13-\dfrac{r}{2a}\right)=0$, so $r=\dfrac{2a}{3}$. At this point,

$$ E_{\max}=\frac{\rho_0}{\varepsilon_0}\left(\frac{2a}{9}-\frac{(2a/3)^2}{4a}\right)=\frac{\rho_0}{\varepsilon_0}\left(\frac{2a}{9}-\frac{a}{9}\right)=\frac{\rho_0a}{9\varepsilon_0} $$

The value at the surface is $E(a)=\dfrac{\rho_0a}{12\varepsilon_0}$, which is smaller than this. Since the density becomes smaller as the surface is approached, the maximum of the electric field occurs not at the surface but in the interior (at $r=2a/3$).

Electric potential: For $r\gt a$,

$$ \varphi(r)=\int_r^\infty\frac{\rho_0a^3}{12\varepsilon_0}\frac1{r'^2}\,\dd r'=\frac{\rho_0a^3}{12\varepsilon_0}\left[-\frac1{r'}\right]_r^\infty=\frac{\rho_0a^3}{12\varepsilon_0\,r}\qquad(r\gt a) $$

For $0\le r\le a$, we split the integration interval into $[r,a]$ (the interior expression) and $[a,\infty)$ (the exterior expression). Since the exterior potential at $r=a$ is $\varphi(a)=\dfrac{\rho_0a^2}{12\varepsilon_0}$,

$$ \varphi(r)=\int_r^a\frac{\rho_0}{\varepsilon_0}\left(\frac{r'}3-\frac{r'^2}{4a}\right)\dd r'+\frac{\rho_0a^2}{12\varepsilon_0}=\frac{\rho_0}{\varepsilon_0}\left[\frac{r'^2}{6}-\frac{r'^3}{12a}\right]_r^a+\frac{\rho_0a^2}{12\varepsilon_0} $$ $$ =\frac{\rho_0}{\varepsilon_0}\left(\frac{a^2}{12}-\frac{r^2}{6}+\frac{r^3}{12a}\right)+\frac{\rho_0a^2}{12\varepsilon_0}=\frac{\rho_0}{12\varepsilon_0a}\left(2a^3-2ar^2+r^3\right)\qquad(0\le r\le a) $$

Comparison with a point charge: Substituting $\rho_0a^3=\dfrac{3Q}{\pi}$ into the exterior potential gives $\varphi(r)=\dfrac{1}{12\varepsilon_0r}\cdot\dfrac{3Q}{\pi}=\dfrac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\dfrac Qr$, which agrees with the potential of a point charge with the total charge $Q$ concentrated at the center (Formula 36.2 of the main text). Also, at $r=a$ the interior and exterior expressions for $E$ and $\varphi$ both give the same values ($\dfrac{\rho_0a}{12\varepsilon_0}$ and $\dfrac{\rho_0a^2}{12\varepsilon_0}$), so they join continuously.

Exercise 36.5: A capacitor with an inserted dielectric (Advanced)

Solution

The charge $Q$ is held constant (the dielectric is inserted with the capacitor disconnected from the external power supply), so from $C=Q/V$ in Definition 36.4 we have $V=Q/C$. Let $C_0$ be the capacitance before the dielectric is inserted, and $V_0=Q/C_0$ the potential difference. Inserting the dielectric makes the capacitance $C=\varepsilon_rC_0$, so

$$ V=\frac{Q}{C}=\frac{Q}{\varepsilon_rC_0}=\frac{V_0}{\varepsilon_r} $$

(1) Therefore, the potential difference becomes $1/\varepsilon_r$ times its value before the dielectric was inserted (since $\varepsilon_r\gt1$, the potential difference decreases).

(2) For the electrostatic energy, we use the form $U=Q^2/(2C)$ (Formula 36.5 of the main text), which is convenient when $Q$ is constant. Before the insertion, $U_0=Q^2/(2C_0)$, and after the insertion,

$$ U=\frac{Q^2}{2C}=\frac{Q^2}{2\varepsilon_rC_0}=\frac{U_0}{\varepsilon_r} $$

Therefore, the electrostatic energy also becomes $1/\varepsilon_r$ times its value before the dielectric was inserted. Only the capacitance increases, while the charge $Q$ (and hence the attraction between the charges on the plates) is unchanged, so both the potential difference and the energy needed to store the same charge become smaller. This is the effect of inserting a dielectric—it helps to understand it with a picture similar to the way the water level drops when the same amount of water is poured into a wider container. The detailed mechanism by which a dielectric increases the capacitance (the weakening of the electric field by polarization) will be studied in Chapter 41.

Exercise 36.6: The relation between the field just next to the face of a charged disk and that of an infinite plane

Solution

The electric field on the axis due to the charge on a disk, found in Example 36.15 of the main text, was, for $x\gt0$,

$$ E(x)=\frac{\sigma}{2\varepsilon_0}\left(1-\frac{x}{\sqrt{a^2+x^2}}\right) $$

Taking $x\to+0$ (the limit of approaching the surface of the disk very closely from the positive side), the numerator of the second term satisfies $x\to0$, so

$$ \lim_{x\to+0}E(x)=\frac{\sigma}{2\varepsilon_0}\left(1-\frac{0}{\sqrt{a^2+0}}\right)=\frac{\sigma}{2\varepsilon_0}(1-0)=\frac{\sigma}{2\varepsilon_0} $$

which agrees exactly with the result $E=\sigma/(2\varepsilon_0)$ of Example 36.4 of the main text (the electric field of an infinitely wide charged plane).

The physical reason can be explained as follows—when the observation point is very close to the surface of the disk (the distance $x$ from the surface being sufficiently small compared with the radius $a$ of the disk), the rim of the disk (its outline of radius $a$) appears to be “very far away” within the range visible from the observation point. That is, even for a disk of finite size, if we look only at the immediate neighborhood of the observation point, it looks as if it were an infinitely wide plane charge, and the electric field also agrees with the infinite-plane result in accordance with this approximation (away from the disk, once $x$ becomes about $a$ or more, the effect of the rim can no longer be neglected, and the field deviates from the infinite-plane result).

The opposite side ($x\lt0$): By symmetry about the plane of the disk, at the point mirrored across the plane the magnitude of the electric field is the same, $\sigma/(2\varepsilon_0)$, and its direction is away from the disk (the $-x$ direction). Therefore, the electric field jumps discontinuously as it crosses the plane of the disk, from $-\sigma/(2\varepsilon_0)$ to $+\sigma/(2\varepsilon_0)$, by a difference of $\sigma/\varepsilon_0$ (the same holds for the infinite plane). Note that the value exactly at the center of the disk, $x=0$, is not determined, and what we found above is a one-sided limit.

Exercise 36.7: Finding the charge density from a spherically symmetric Gaussian potential

Solution

From Poisson's equation $\nabla^2\varphi=-\rho/\varepsilon_0$ we have $\rho=-\varepsilon_0\nabla^2\varphi$, and since the system is spherically symmetric we use $\nabla^2\varphi=\dfrac1{r^2}\dfrac{\dd}{\dd r}\left(r^2\dfrac{\dd\varphi}{\dd r}\right)$. First, differentiating $\varphi=V_0\exp(-r^2/a^2)$ with respect to $r$ (differentiation of a composite function), we obtain

$$ \frac{\dd\varphi}{\dd r}=V_0\cdot\left(-\frac{2r}{a^2}\right)\exp(-r^2/a^2)=-\frac{2r}{a^2}\varphi $$

Multiply by $r^2$ and differentiate once more with respect to $r$ (using the product rule):

$$ \frac{\dd}{\dd r}\left(r^2\frac{\dd\varphi}{\dd r}\right)=\frac{\dd}{\dd r}\left(-\frac{2r^3}{a^2}\varphi\right)=-\frac{6r^2}{a^2}\varphi-\frac{2r^3}{a^2}\cdot\left(-\frac{2r}{a^2}\right)\varphi=\left(-\frac{6r^2}{a^2}+\frac{4r^4}{a^4}\right)\varphi $$

Dividing by $r^2$ gives $\nabla^2\varphi=\left(-\dfrac{6}{a^2}+\dfrac{4r^2}{a^4}\right)\varphi$, so

$$ \rho(r)=-\varepsilon_0\nabla^2\varphi=\varepsilon_0V_0\left(\frac{6}{a^2}-\frac{4r^2}{a^4}\right)\exp(-r^2/a^2) $$

(unit check: $\varepsilon_0V_0/a^2$ has units $\dfrac{\mathrm{F}}{\mathrm m}\cdot\dfrac{\mathrm V}{\mathrm{m^2}}=\dfrac{\mathrm C}{\mathrm{m^3}}$, which agrees with the unit of charge density).

Total charge: Since the system is spherically symmetric, we take $\dd V=4\pi r^2\,\dd r$, so that

$$ Q_{\text{total}}=4\pi\int_0^\infty\rho\,r^2\,\dd r=4\pi\varepsilon_0V_0\left[\frac{6}{a^2}\int_0^\infty r^2\exp(-r^2/a^2)\dd r-\frac{4}{a^4}\int_0^\infty r^4\exp(-r^2/a^2)\dd r\right] $$

Substituting the values of the integrals given in the hint, we have $\dfrac{6}{a^2}\cdot\dfrac{\sqrt\pi}{4}a^3=\dfrac{3\sqrt\pi}{2}a$ and $\dfrac{4}{a^4}\cdot\dfrac{3\sqrt\pi}{8}a^5=\dfrac{3\sqrt\pi}{2}a$, so

$$ Q_{\text{total}}=4\pi\varepsilon_0V_0\left(\frac{3\sqrt\pi}{2}a-\frac{3\sqrt\pi}{2}a\right)=0 $$

Physically, $\rho(r)$ is positive near the center ($r\lt\sqrt{3/2}\,a$) and negative outside it, and the distribution as a whole is electrically neutral. The reason the potential approaches $0$ at large distances in a Gaussian fashion (far faster than $1/r$) is that, seen from far away, the total charge is $0$ (no electric field of the $1/r^2$ form remains).

← Back to Chapter 36, “Electric Potential and Capacitors”

Chapter 37 Steady Current and Electric Circuits

Exercise 37.1: Dimensional analysis of the two expressions for current, and the speed of electrons in a thin copper wire

Solution

(1) The right-hand side of $I=\dd Q/\dd t$ is “the electric charge divided by the time,” so its unit is

$$ \left[\frac{\dd Q}{\dd t}\right]=\frac{[\mathrm C]}{[\mathrm s]}=\mathrm{C/s} $$

On the other hand, the right-hand side of $I=envS$ is the product of $e$ (electric charge, unit $\mathrm C$), $n$ (number per $1\ \mathrm m^3$, unit $\mathrm{1/m^3}$), $v$ (speed, unit $\mathrm{m/s}$), and $S$ (area, unit $\mathrm{m^2}$), so multiplying the units together one by one gives

$$ [envS]=\mathrm C\times\frac{1}{\mathrm m^3}\times\frac{\mathrm m}{\mathrm s}\times\mathrm m^2 =\mathrm C\times\frac{\mathrm m^3}{\mathrm m^3}\times\frac{1}{\mathrm s} =\mathrm{C/s} $$

(Counting the powers of $\mathrm m$: the numerator has $\mathrm m^1\times\mathrm m^2=\mathrm m^3$ and the denominator has $\mathrm m^3$, so they cancel exactly.) Both have the unit $\mathrm{C/s}=\mathrm A$ (ampere, Definition 37.1), which confirms that the two ways of expressing current represent quantities of the same dimension. Note also that, for the third expression $I=\iint_S\bm j\cdot\bm n\,\dd S$, since the unit of the current density $\bm j$ is $[\mathrm{A/m^2}]$ (Definition 37.2 in Section 37.2), multiplying by an area in $\mathrm m^2$ gives $\mathrm A$, which is again consistent.

(2) The cross-sectional area for a diameter of $d=0.50\ \mathrm{mm}$ is

$$ S=\pi\left(\frac d2\right)^2=\pi\times(0.25\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm m)^2\approx1.96\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{m^2} $$

Because the current is perpendicular to the cross section and uniform, the magnitude of the current density is

$$ j=\frac IS=\frac{0.50\ \mathrm A}{1.96\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{m^2}}\approx2.5\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{A/m^2} $$

From $j=env$, the average velocity is

$$ v=\frac{j}{en}=\frac{2.55\times10^{6}}{(1.602\times10^{-19})\times(8.49\times10^{28})}\approx1.9\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

This is faster than in the thick copper wire of Example 37.1 ($7.4\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{m/s}$), but it is still only about $0.2\ \mathrm{mm/s}$. The thinner the wire, the faster the electrons must move to carry the same current.

Exercise 37.2: Current through a tilted cross section

Solution

(1) Let the area of the cross section perpendicular to the axis be $S=1.0\ \mathrm{mm^2}$. When the normal $\bm n$ of the cut makes an angle $\theta=30^\circ$ with the axis (the direction of $\bm j$), the area of the cut is $S'=S/\cos\theta$ (the width of the wire does not change, and the cut becomes longer because it is slanted). Therefore,

$$ S'=\frac{1.0\ \mathrm{mm^2}}{\cos30^\circ}=\frac{1.0}{0.8660}\ \mathrm{mm^2}\approx1.15\ \mathrm{mm^2} $$

(2) On the cut, $\bm j\cdot\bm n=j\cos\theta=(2.0\times10^{6})\times0.8660\approx1.73\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{A/m^2}$, and since $\bm j$ is uniform over the whole cut, the surface integral reduces to a simple product:

$$ I'=\iint_{S'}\bm j\cdot\bm n\,\dd S=(j\cos\theta)\,S'=(1.73\times10^{6})\times(1.155\times10^{-6})\approx2.0\ \mathrm A $$

On the other hand, the current through the perpendicular cross section is $I=jS=(2.0\times10^{6})\times(1.0\times10^{-6})=2.0\ \mathrm A$, so $I'=I$, and the two agree.

(3) For a general $\theta$,

$$ I'=(j\cos\theta)\,S'=(j\cos\theta)\,\frac{S}{\cos\theta}=jS=I $$

so the $\cos\theta$ factors cancel, and the current is the same regardless of the inclination $\theta$. This is a check, in the simplest case, of the derivation in Section 37.2 of the main text (using $\nabla\cdot\bm j=0$ and the divergence theorem) that the currents through two cross sections of a wire are equal.

Exercise 37.3: Temperature dependence of resistivity (Advanced)

Solution

In the Drude model of Section 37.3 of the main text, the resistivity is expressed as

$$ \rho=\frac{m_{\mathrm e}}{ne^2\tau} $$

($\tau$: the relaxation time, the average time interval between scatterings of a free electron by positive ions and the like). When the temperature of a metal rises, the positive ions undergo more violent thermal vibration about their lattice sites. From the free electron's point of view, the more strongly the ions vibrate, the higher the probability that the free electron is scattered—both the distance it can travel and the time it can go between one collision and the next become shorter. Therefore, as the temperature rises, the relaxation time $\tau$ becomes shorter.

Note that $\tau$ is in the denominator of the expression for $\rho$: if $\tau$ becomes shorter (assuming that the other quantities $m_{\mathrm e},n,e$ do not change), $\rho$ becomes larger. This is why the resistivity of a metal usually increases with temperature.

This explanation implicitly relies on the assumption that “the free-electron density $n$ is almost independent of temperature.” If $n$ also changed greatly with temperature, whether $\rho=m_{\mathrm e}/(ne^2\tau)$ increases or decreases would be decided by the balance between the changes in both $n$ and $\tau$, and we could no longer simply say that “$\tau$ decreases, so $\rho$ increases.” In a metal, the free electrons originate from the conduction electrons (valence electrons) released by each atom, and their total number (= the free-electron density $n$) is hardly affected by the arrangement of the atoms or the intensity of their thermal vibration—this is because the atoms merely vibrate while staying in place, and whether or not they release electrons does not change greatly with temperature. In contrast, in a semiconductor, the free electrons (conduction-band electrons) are newly created by thermal excitation, and as the temperature rises $n$ itself increases sharply, in an exponential manner, so this assumption does not hold, and as a result the resistivity often decreases with temperature.

Quantitative part: substituting $\alpha=4\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{K^{-1}}$ and $T-T_0=100-20=80\ \mathrm K$ into $\rho(T)=\rho_0[1+\alpha(T-T_0)]$ gives $\rho(100\ ^\circ\mathrm C)=\rho_0\times(1+4\times10^{-3}\times80)=1.32\,\rho_0$. If the shape of the wire ($l,S$) does not change, the resistance is also multiplied by $1.32$, so

$$ R(100\ ^\circ\mathrm C)=0.168\ \Omega\times1.32\approx0.222\ \Omega $$
