Also, if $n$ is constant, then $\rho\propto1/\tau$, so $\tau(100\ ^\circ\mathrm C)/\tau(20\ ^\circ\mathrm C)=1/1.32\approx0.76$. Using $\tau=2.49\times10^{-14}\ \mathrm s$ from Example 37.6, at $100\ ^\circ\mathrm C$ it becomes about $1.9\times10^{-14}\ \mathrm s$.

Exercise 37.4: Energy check of the series and parallel formulas

Solution

(a) Series connection. By Formula 37.2, the equivalent resistance is $R=R_1+R_2=6.0+3.0=9.0\ \Omega$. Since the connection is in series, the same current flows through each of $R_1,R_2$, and the total current flowing out of the source equals it:

$$ I=I_1=I_2=\frac{V}{R}=\frac{12}{9.0}=\frac43\ \mathrm A\approx1.33\ \mathrm A $$

The power dissipated in each resistor is, by Formula 37.1 ($P=I^2R$),

$$ P_1=I^2R_1=\left(\frac43\right)^2\times6.0\approx10.7\ \mathrm W,\qquad P_2=I^2R_2=\left(\frac43\right)^2\times3.0\approx5.33\ \mathrm W $$

The total power supplied by the source is $P=VI=12\times\dfrac43=16\ \mathrm W$, which agrees with $P_1+P_2=10.7+5.33=16\ \mathrm W$. (Using $P=V^2/R=144/9.0=16\ \mathrm W$ gives the same result.)

(b) Parallel connection. Since the connection is in parallel, the same voltage $V=12\ \mathrm V$ is applied across each of $R_1,R_2$ (Section 37.5.2 of the main text). By Ohm's law,

$$ I_1=\frac{V}{R_1}=\frac{12}{6.0}=2.0\ \mathrm A,\qquad I_2=\frac{V}{R_2}=\frac{12}{3.0}=4.0\ \mathrm A $$

The power dissipated in each resistor is, by Formula 37.1 ($P=V^2/R$),

$$ P_1=\frac{V^2}{R_1}=\frac{12^2}{6.0}=24\ \mathrm W,\qquad P_2=\frac{V^2}{R_2}=\frac{12^2}{3.0}=48\ \mathrm W $$

Next, by Formula 37.3 (the equivalent resistance of a parallel connection, in its two-term closed form),

$$ R=\frac{R_1R_2}{R_1+R_2}=\frac{6.0\times3.0}{6.0+3.0}=\frac{18}{9.0}=2.0\ \Omega $$

The total current flowing out of the source is $I=V/R=12/2.0=6.0\ \mathrm A$ (this also agrees with $I_1+I_2=2.0+4.0=6.0\ \mathrm A$, and is a concrete example of Kirchhoff's first law—Law 37.3 of the main text). The total power supplied by the source is, by Formula 37.1 ($P=IV$),

$$ P=IV=6.0\times12=72\ \mathrm W $$

On the other hand, $P_1+P_2=24+48=72\ \mathrm W$, which confirms that $P=P_1+P_2$ holds.

In both connections, the energy supplied by the source equals the simple sum of the energy dissipated in the individual resistors (the energy conservation law). The formulas for the equivalent resistance were derived from the sum of voltages in the series case and the sum of currents in the parallel case (Kirchhoff's laws), and this check shows that, as a result, they are also free of contradiction in the energy balance. For the same voltage, the parallel connection ($R=2.0\ \Omega$) dissipates more power ($72\ \mathrm W$ versus $16\ \mathrm W$) than the series connection ($R=9.0\ \Omega$).

Exercise 37.5: Solving a circuit with Kirchhoff's laws

Solution

We use the same setup and the same choice of current directions as Example 37.9 of the main text (in every battery, the upper side is the positive terminal). Let $I_1$ be the current flowing into node N from the left branch, $I_3$ the current flowing into it from the right branch, and $I_2$ the current flowing out of node N through the central branch ($R_2$). By Kirchhoff's first law (Law 37.3) of Chapter 37,

$$ I_1+I_3=I_2 \qquad\cdots\ \text{(a)} $$

Applying the second law (Law 37.4) to the left loop (containing $\mathcal E_1,R_1,R_2$), starting from the grounded point, we get $+\mathcal E_1-I_1R_1-I_2R_2-\mathcal E_2=0$. Here $\mathcal E_2=0\ \mathrm V$ (the central branch has no battery, only the resistor $R_2$), so

$$ \mathcal E_1-\mathcal E_2=I_1R_1+I_2R_2 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \mathcal E_1=I_1R_1+I_2R_2 \qquad\cdots\ \text{(b)} $$

In the same way for the right loop (containing $\mathcal E_3,R_3,R_2$),

$$ \mathcal E_3-\mathcal E_2=I_3R_3+I_2R_2 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \mathcal E_3=I_3R_3+I_2R_2 \qquad\cdots\ \text{(c)} $$

Substituting the numerical values ($R_1=4\ \Omega,R_2=2\ \Omega,R_3=6\ \Omega,\mathcal E_1=8\ \mathrm V,\mathcal E_3=5\ \mathrm V$), (b) becomes $8=4I_1+2I_2$ and (c) becomes $5=6I_3+2I_2$. Solving the three simultaneous linear equations (a), (b), (c) in three unknowns gives

$$ I_1=\frac{27}{22}\approx1.227\ \mathrm A,\qquad I_2=\frac{17}{11}\approx1.545\ \mathrm A,\qquad I_3=\frac{7}{22}\approx0.318\ \mathrm A $$

Since $I_1,I_2,I_3$ are all positive, the directions assumed at the outset ($I_1,I_3$ flowing into node N, and $I_2$ flowing out of node N) are the actual current directions. As a check, substituting into (a) gives $I_1+I_3=27/22+7/22=34/22=17/11=I_2$, which indeed holds.

Exercise 37.6: Deriving the continuity equation using an infinitesimal rectangular box

Solution

Consider an infinitesimal rectangular box whose edges, of lengths $\Delta x,\Delta y,\Delta z$, are parallel to the coordinate axes. Let one of its vertices be $(x,y,z)$, and focus on the faces perpendicular to the $x$ direction (the two faces parallel to the $y$-$z$ plane). The current flowing in through the face on the smaller-$x$ side (area $\Delta y\Delta z$) is, in terms of the $x$ component $j_x(x,y,z,t)$ of the current density at that face,

$$ j_x(x,y,z,t)\,\Delta y\Delta z $$

The current flowing out through the face on the $x+\Delta x$ side is, because $\Delta x$ is an infinitesimal quantity, approximated by the first-order Taylor expansion (University Mathematics, the first-order approximation of a function of several variables) as

$$ j_x(x+\Delta x,y,z,t)\,\Delta y\Delta z\approx\left[j_x(x,y,z,t)+\pdiff{j_x}{x}\Delta x\right]\Delta y\Delta z $$

Therefore, the “net outflow” (outflow minus inflow) when we look only at the two faces in the $x$ direction is

$$ \left[j_x(x,y,z,t)+\pdiff{j_x}{x}\Delta x\right]\Delta y\Delta z-j_x(x,y,z,t)\,\Delta y\Delta z=\pdiff{j_x}{x}\,\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z $$

Exactly the same holds for the $y$ and $z$ directions, and the net outflows are $\pdiff{j_y}{y}\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z$ and $\pdiff{j_z}{z}\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z$, respectively. The total net outflow through all six faces of the box is the sum over these three directions,

$$ \left(\pdiff{j_x}{x}+\pdiff{j_y}{y}+\pdiff{j_z}{z}\right)\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z=(\nabla\cdot\bm j)\,\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z $$

($\nabla\cdot\bm j=\partial j_x/\partial x+\partial j_y/\partial y+\partial j_z/\partial z$ is precisely the definition of the divergence that we learned in University Mathematics, Section 19.1). On the other hand, as in the argument of the main text, this net outflow equals the rate of decrease of the charge $Q=\rho\,\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z$ inside the box ($\rho$ can be regarded as almost constant within the box because the volume is infinitesimal):

$$ (\nabla\cdot\bm j)\,\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z=-\frac{\dd Q}{\dd t}=-\pdiff{\rho}{t}\,\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z $$

Dividing both sides by the common volume $\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z$ gives

$$ \nabla\cdot\bm j=-\pdiff{\rho}{t} $$

and we obtain, from the infinitesimal box (a local argument), exactly the same result as the continuity equation (Law 37.1) that was derived in Section 37.2 of the main text using the divergence theorem (a global argument for a closed surface). In fact, Gauss's divergence theorem is precisely “the relation that holds for each infinitesimal box, summed over the entire region,” and the argument of this problem is itself the skeleton of the proof of the divergence theorem (see “Physical Meaning of Divergence” in University Mathematics, Section 19.2).

Exercise 37.7: The time constant of an RC circuit and the speed of charging

Solution

By Definition 37.5 of the main text, the time constant is

$$ \tau_{\mathrm{RC}}=RC=(2.0\times10^{3}\ \Omega)\times(100\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm F)=0.20\ \mathrm s $$

The voltage of the capacitor during charging is obtained by dividing the main text's expression for charging, $Q(t)=C\mathcal E(1-\exp(-t/\tau_{\mathrm{RC}}))$, by $C$:

$$ V_C(t)=\mathcal E\left(1-\exp(-t/\tau_{\mathrm{RC}})\right) $$

Substituting $t=0.10\ \mathrm s$ and $\tau_{\mathrm{RC}}=0.20\ \mathrm s$, we have $t/\tau_{\mathrm{RC}}=0.10/0.20=0.50$, so

$$ V_C(0.10)=5.0\times\left(1-\exp(-0.50)\right)=5.0\times(1-0.6065)=5.0\times0.3935\approx1.97\ \mathrm V $$

Next, for discharge from the fully charged state (voltage $\mathcal E$) through the resistor alone, dividing the main text's expression for discharging, $Q(t)=Q_0\exp(-t/\tau_{\mathrm{RC}})$, by $C$ gives

$$ V_C(t)=\mathcal E\,\exp(-t/\tau_{\mathrm{RC}}) $$

The time $t_{1/2}$ at which the voltage drops to half is found by setting $V_C(t_{1/2})=\mathcal E/2$ and solving

$$ \frac12=\exp(-t_{1/2}/\tau_{\mathrm{RC}}) \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad t_{1/2}=\tau_{\mathrm{RC}}\ln2 $$

(we took the natural logarithm of both sides and used $\ln(1/2)=-\ln2$). Substituting the numerical values,

$$ t_{1/2}=0.20\times\ln2\approx0.20\times0.6931\approx0.139\ \mathrm s $$

The relation $t_{1/2}=\tau_{\mathrm{RC}}\ln2$ comes from the same structure as the half-life of radioactive decay: “after each equal interval of time, the remaining quantity decreases by the same fraction.”

Exercise 37.8: Joule heating and temperature rise of a copper wire

Solution

(1) The heat generated per unit volume per unit time is $p=\bm j\cdot\bm E=\rho j^2$ (Section 37.4 of the main text):

$$ p=\rho j^2=(1.68\times10^{-8}\ \Omega\cdot\mathrm m)\times(1.0\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{A/m^2})^2=1.68\times10^{4}\ \mathrm{W/m^3} $$

(unit check: $\Omega\cdot\mathrm m\cdot\mathrm{A^2/m^4}=(\mathrm{V/A})\,\mathrm A^2/\mathrm m^3=\mathrm{W/m^3}$ is correct).

(2) The heat capacity of copper per unit volume is (density) × (specific heat) $=(8.96\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{kg/m^3})\times(385\ \mathrm{J/(kg\cdot K)})\approx3.45\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{J/(m^3\cdot K)}$. In the adiabatic case, all of the heat generation $p$ is used for the temperature rise, so

$$ \frac{\dd T}{\dd t}=\frac{p}{3.45\times10^{6}}=\frac{1.68\times10^{4}}{3.45\times10^{6}}\approx4.9\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{K/s} $$

and over $1$ minute ($60\ \mathrm s$), $\Delta T\approx0.29\ \mathrm K$.

(3) In a real wire, heat escapes from the surface to the surrounding air (heat dissipation), so the dissipation increases as the temperature rises, and the temperature settles at the value at which heat generation and heat dissipation balance. Since the heat generation is proportional to the volume of the wire and the heat dissipation to its surface area, a thicker wire cools less easily even at the same current density. This is why the allowable current is determined by the thickness of the wire and the heat-resistance temperature of its insulation.

Exercise 37.9: Internal resistance of a battery and maximum power

Solution

(1) Since the internal resistance $r$ and the external resistance $R$ are in series, the total resistance is $R+r$, and by Kirchhoff's second law $\mathcal E=IR+Ir$, that is,

$$ I=\frac{\mathcal E}{R+r} $$

The terminal voltage is obtained by subtracting the voltage drop $Ir$ across the battery's internal resistance:

$$ V=\mathcal E-Ir=\mathcal E-\frac{\mathcal E r}{R+r}=\frac{R}{R+r}\,\mathcal E $$

(This equals the voltage $IR$ across the external resistance $R$, and is also the voltage-divider formula $V=R\mathcal E/(R+r)$.) The more current is drawn, the further the terminal voltage falls below the electromotive force (emf).

(2) The power dissipated in the external resistance $R$ is

$$ P_R=I^2R=\frac{\mathcal E^2R}{(R+r)^2} $$

Differentiating with respect to $R$,

$$ \frac{\dd P_R}{\dd R}=\mathcal E^2\,\frac{(R+r)^2-2R(R+r)}{(R+r)^4}=\mathcal E^2\,\frac{r-R}{(R+r)^3} $$

This is positive for $R\lt r$ and negative for $R\gt r$, so $P_R$ is maximum at $R=r$, and the maximum value is

$$ P_{\max}=\frac{\mathcal E^2r}{(2r)^2}=\frac{\mathcal E^2}{4r} $$

(When the load resistance is matched to the internal resistance of the battery, the largest power can be extracted. In that case half of the power becomes heat in the internal resistance $r$, so the efficiency is $50\%$.)

(3) For $\mathcal E=1.5\ \mathrm V$ and $r=0.50\ \Omega$: with $R=0.50\ \Omega$, $I=1.5/(0.50+0.50)=1.5\ \mathrm A$ and $P_R=I^2R=1.5^2\times0.50=1.125\ \mathrm W\approx1.1\ \mathrm W$ (which agrees with $\mathcal E^2/(4r)=2.25/2.0=1.125\ \mathrm W$). With $R=2.0\ \Omega$, $I=1.5/2.5=0.60\ \mathrm A$ and $P_R=0.60^2\times2.0=0.72\ \mathrm W$. The power is larger for $R=r$.

← Back to Chapter 37, “Steady Current and Electric Circuits”

Chapter 38 Sources of the Magnetic Field — The Biot–Savart Law and Ampère's Law

Exercise 38.1: Direction of the cross product in the Biot–Savart law

Solution

The Biot–Savart law is $\dd\bm H=\dfrac{1}{4\pi r^2}\left(I\dd\bm\ell\times\hat{\bm r}\right)$. Since the current element points in the $+x$ direction and the direction to the observation point is the $+y$ direction, we can write $I\dd\bm\ell=I\dd\ell\,\bm e_x$ and $\hat{\bm r}=\bm e_y$. For the cross product of unit vectors, in a right-handed system (a coordinate system in which the $x,y,z$ axes are taken along the directions of the thumb, index finger, and middle finger of the right hand, in this order) we have $\bm e_x\times\bm e_y=\bm e_z$. Therefore,

$$ \dd\bm H=\frac{I\dd\ell}{4\pi r^2}\,\bm e_x\times\bm e_y=\frac{I\dd\ell}{4\pi r^2}\,\bm e_z $$

which shows that $\dd\bm H$ points in the $+z$ direction. Checking with the right hand: when the fingers of the right hand are curled from the direction of $\dd\bm\ell$ ($+x$) toward the direction of $\hat{\bm r}$ ($+y$), the thumb points in the $+z$ direction (turning a right-handed screw from the $x$ axis toward the $y$ axis advances the screw in the positive direction of the $z$ axis). Also, since $\dd\bm\ell\perp\hat{\bm r}$ ($\theta=90^\circ$, $\sin\theta=1$), the magnitude is $\dd H=\dfrac{I\dd\ell}{4\pi r^2}$, and $\dd\bm H$ is perpendicular to both $\dd\bm\ell$ and $\bm r$.

To draw the figure, proceed as follows. Take the $x$ axis rightward, the $y$ axis upward, and the $z$ axis pointing out of the page toward the viewer (a right-handed system). From the current element at the origin, draw $\dd\bm\ell$ as an arrow pointing right ($+x$) and $\hat{\bm r}$, directed toward the observation point, as an arrow pointing up ($+y$); at the observation point, draw $\dd\bm H$ as an arrow pointing out of the page toward the viewer ($+z$) (the symbol $\odot$, a dot enclosed in a circle). That the thumb points toward the viewer when the fingers of the right hand are curled from rightward to upward corresponds to the relationship among these three arrows.

∎

Exercise 38.2: Magnetic field at the center of a square loop

Solution

The foot of the perpendicular dropped from the center of the square onto each side is the midpoint of that side. The distance from the center to that foot is $r=L/2$, and the two ends of each side are at $\pm L/2$ from the foot, so the half-length is $a=L/2$. Substituting $a=r=L/2$ into Formula 38.2 of Chapter 38 (the magnetic field of a wire of finite length, $H=\dfrac{I}{2\pi r}\sin\left(\tan^{-1}\dfrac ar\right)$), the magnitude of the magnetic field produced by one side is

$$ H_1=\frac{I}{2\pi\cdot\frac L2}\sin\left(\tan^{-1}1\right)=\frac{I}{\pi L}\sin\frac\pi4=\frac{I}{\pi L}\cdot\frac{\sqrt2}{2}=\frac{\sqrt2\,I}{2\pi L} $$

(here $\tan^{-1}1=\pi/4$ and $\sin(\pi/4)=\sqrt2/2$). When the current flows counterclockwise, by the Biot–Savart law (the right-hand rule), the magnetic field produced at the center by the current element on any of the four sides is perpendicular to the page and in the same direction (out of the page toward the viewer; as in the case of a circular current, for each current element the direction of the cross product of the vector from the side to the center and the direction of the current is the same for all sides). Therefore, the four contributions, which all have the same direction, can simply be added, and

$$ H=4H_1=4\times\frac{\sqrt2\,I}{2\pi L}=\frac{2\sqrt2\,I}{\pi L}\approx0.900\,\frac{I}{L} $$

As a check, the magnetic field at the center of a circular current inscribed in the square (radius $r=L/2$) is $\dfrac{I}{2r}=\dfrac{I}{L}$, and the value for the square is slightly weaker than that (about $90\ \%$), which is consistent with the fact that the corners add portions that are farther from the center.

∎

Exercise 38.3: Magnetic field of a semi-infinite straight current

Solution

Take a coordinate $x$ along the wire, with the endpoint at the origin O and the wire on the $x\ge0$ side. The observation point is a point P at distance $r$ from the endpoint, measured perpendicular to the wire. For this point the endpoint is the “foot of the perpendicular,” and with the same geometry as in Section 38.3 (let $\theta$ be the angle that the line from P to the current element makes with the perpendicular; then $x=r\tan\theta$, the distance from the current element to P is $r/\cos\theta$, and the angle between the direction of the current and $\bm r$ is $\theta+\pi/2$), the magnitude of the magnetic field produced by a current element is

$$ \dd H=\frac{I\cos^3\theta}{4\pi r^2}\,\dd x=\frac{I}{4\pi r}\cos\theta\,\dd\theta $$

(we substituted $\dd x=\dfrac{r}{\cos^2\theta}\dd\theta$). Since the wire extends from $x=0$ to $x=\infty$, the angle $\theta$ satisfies $\theta=0$ when $x=0$ and $\theta\to\pi/2$ as $x\to\infty$. Therefore,

$$ H=\int_0^{\pi/2}\frac{I}{4\pi r}\cos\theta\,\dd\theta=\frac{I}{4\pi r}\Bigl[\sin\theta\Bigr]_0^{\pi/2}=\frac{I}{4\pi r}\,(1-0)=\frac{I}{4\pi r} $$

This is exactly half of the magnetic field $\dfrac{I}{2\pi r}$ of an infinitely long wire at the same distance $r$. Thinking in terms of symmetry, an infinitely long wire can be regarded as two semi-infinite wires joined at their endpoints, each producing a magnetic field of the same magnitude and the same direction, so this is a natural result. Note also that at a point on the extension of the wire, $\dd\bm\ell\parallel\bm r$ ($\sin\theta=0$), so the magnetic field is $0$.

∎

Exercise 38.4: Uniformity of the magnetic field inside a solenoid (Advanced)

Solution

Let $R$ be the radius of the solenoid, and in cylindrical coordinates centered on the axis suppose that the magnetic field inside ($s\lt R$) is parallel to the axis with magnitude $H(s)$. Consider a rectangular path consisting of two sides of length $\ell$ parallel to the axis, at distances $s_1,s_2$ from the axis (both less than $R$), joined by two sides perpendicular to the axis (in the radial direction). This rectangle fits entirely inside the solenoid and encloses no winding at all, so $I_{\text{enc}}=0$.

On the two sides perpendicular to the axis, the magnetic field (parallel to the axis) and the path (radial) are orthogonal, so $\bm H\cdot\dd\bm\ell=0$ and the contribution is $0$. On the two sides parallel to the axis, going once around the rectangle, one side is traversed in the same direction as the magnetic field and the other in the opposite direction, so

$$ \oint_C\bm H\cdot\dd\bm\ell=H(s_1)\,\ell-H(s_2)\,\ell=I_{\text{enc}}=0\qquad\therefore\ H(s_1)=H(s_2) $$

Since $s_1,s_2$ are arbitrary values inside the solenoid, the magnetic field inside is uniform, independent of the distance from the axis.

Next, we find the magnitude of the magnetic field inside by means of a different path. Of the two sides of the rectangle parallel to the axis, take one (of length $\ell$, traversed in the same direction as the magnetic field) at a distance $s_1=R/2$ inside, and the other (traversed in the direction opposite to the magnetic field) at a distance $s_2\gt R$ outside the solenoid. The magnetic field outside is $0$ (the Discussion in Section 38.4), and the contributions of the two sides perpendicular to the axis are $0$, so $\oint_C\bm H\cdot\dd\bm\ell=H\ell$. The current enclosed by this path is the current $n\ell I$ flowing in the $n\ell$ wires in the interval of length $\ell$. By Ampère's law, $H\ell=n\ell I$, that is, $H=nI$, which is the same result as in the main text (Figure 38.11). Since $\ell$ cancels from both sides, the result does not change however the length $\ell$ of the rectangle is chosen. Also, the same result is obtained wherever the inner side is placed inside the solenoid, that is, at any distance $s_1$ (because the magnetic field inside is uniform, as shown above).

∎

Exercise 38.5: Checking the dimensions of the magnetic field formulas

Solution

Consider the unit of the right-hand side of the straight-current formula $H=\dfrac{I}{2\pi r}$. Since $2\pi$ is a dimensionless number, the unit is $[I]/[r]=\mathrm{A}/\mathrm{m}$. In the solenoid formula $H=nI$, $n$ is the “number of turns per unit length,” and since a number of turns is a (dimensionless) number, $[n]=\mathrm{1/m}$; multiplying this by $[I]=\mathrm A$ gives $[nI]=\dfrac{1}{\mathrm m}\cdot\mathrm A=\mathrm{A/m}$. The right-hand sides of both formulas are in $\mathrm{A/m}$, which agrees with the unit of the magnetic field $H$ ($[\mathrm{N/Wb}]=[\mathrm{A/m}]$, derived from Definition 38.2 and confirmed by an equation in Chapter 38).

Next, we examine the unit of $B=\mu_0H$. Since $[\mu_0]=\mathrm{N/A^2}$ and $[H]=\mathrm{A/m}$,

$$ [B]=\frac{\mathrm N}{\mathrm A^2}\cdot\frac{\mathrm A}{\mathrm m}=\frac{\mathrm N}{\mathrm A\,\mathrm m} $$

On the other hand, using $[\mathrm{Wb}]=\dfrac{\mathrm N\,\mathrm m}{\mathrm A}$ (an equation in Chapter 38), we obtain

$$ \frac{[\mathrm{Wb}]}{\mathrm m^2}=\frac{\mathrm N\,\mathrm m}{\mathrm A\,\mathrm m^2}=\frac{\mathrm N}{\mathrm A\,\mathrm m} $$

and hence $[\mathrm{N/(A\,m)}]=[\mathrm{Wb/m^2}]=[\mathrm T]$ is verified.

∎

Exercise 38.6: The Helmholtz coil (Advanced)

Solution

(1) The magnetic flux density at a point on the central axis of a single circular coil ($N$ turns, current $I$), at axial distance $u$ from the center of the coil, is given by the following, obtained by multiplying Formula 38.5 ($H(z)=\dfrac{Ia^2}{2(a^2+z^2)^{3/2}}$) by $N$ and setting $B=\mu_0H$:

$$ B_1(u)=\frac{\mu_0NIa^2}{2\,(a^2+u^2)^{3/2}}=\frac{\mu_0NIa^2}{2}\,f(u),\qquad f(u)=(a^2+u^2)^{-3/2} $$

Placing the centers of the two coils at $z=\mp d/2$, the magnetic flux density at the point $z$ on the axis is the sum of the contributions of the two coils (which have the same direction), namely

$$ B(z)=\frac{\mu_0NIa^2}{2}\left[f\!\left(z-\tfrac d2\right)+f\!\left(z+\tfrac d2\right)\right] $$

The midpoint is $z=0$, and for $d=a$, $f(\pm a/2)=\left(a^2+\dfrac{a^2}{4}\right)^{-3/2}=\left(\dfrac54\right)^{-3/2}a^{-3}$, so that

$$ B(0)=\frac{\mu_0NIa^2}{2}\times2\times\left(\frac45\right)^{3/2}\frac{1}{a^3}=\left(\frac45\right)^{3/2}\frac{\mu_0NI}{a}=\frac{8}{5\sqrt5}\,\frac{\mu_0NI}{a}\approx0.716\,\frac{\mu_0NI}{a} $$

This is the value of the magnetic flux density at the midpoint.

(2) Since $f(u)$ is an even function, $f'(u)$ is an odd function and $f''(u)$ is an even function. Differentiating $B(z)$ with respect to $z$ and setting $z=0$ gives

$$ B'(0)=\frac{\mu_0NIa^2}{2}\left[f'\!\left(-\tfrac d2\right)+f'\!\left(\tfrac d2\right)\right]=0 $$

(the two terms cancel because the derivative is an odd function). The second derivative is

$$ B''(0)=\frac{\mu_0NIa^2}{2}\left[f''\!\left(-\tfrac d2\right)+f''\!\left(\tfrac d2\right)\right]=\mu_0NIa^2\,f''\!\left(\tfrac d2\right) $$

Differentiating $f'(u)=-3u(a^2+u^2)^{-5/2}$ once more with the product rule gives

$$ f''(u)=-3(a^2+u^2)^{-5/2}+15u^2(a^2+u^2)^{-7/2}=\frac{-3(a^2+u^2)+15u^2}{(a^2+u^2)^{7/2}}=\frac{3(4u^2-a^2)}{(a^2+u^2)^{7/2}} $$

Substituting $u=d/2$, we have $4u^2=d^2$, so

$$ B''(0)=\mu_0NIa^2\cdot\frac{3(d^2-a^2)}{\left(a^2+\frac{d^2}{4}\right)^{7/2}} $$

This becomes $0$ if and only if $d^2=a^2$, that is, $d=a$ ($d\gt0$). Since $B(z)$ is an even function of $z$ (the two coils are symmetric about the origin), the expansion has the form $B(z)=B(0)+\dfrac12B''(0)z^2+\dfrac1{24}B''''(0)z^4+\cdots$. When $d=a$, we have $B''(0)=0$, so the $z^2$ term also vanishes, and the $z^4$ term is the first correction. For this reason the magnetic field is very uniform near the midpoint.

(3) For $a=0.15\ \mathrm m$, $N=130$, and $I=1.0\ \mathrm A$, we get

$$ B(0)=\left(\frac45\right)^{3/2}\times\frac{(1.2566\times10^{-6})\times130\times1.0}{0.15}\approx0.7155\times1.089\times10^{-3}\approx7.79\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm T=0.78\ \mathrm{mT} $$

The magnetic flux density at the center of a single coil of the same kind, obtained by multiplying Formula 38.3 by $N$, is $B=\mu_0\dfrac{NI}{2a}=\dfrac{(1.2566\times10^{-6})\times130\times1.0}{2\times0.15}\approx5.45\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm T=0.54\ \mathrm{mT}$, so the Helmholtz coil produces a magnetic field about $1.43$ times ($=2\times(4/5)^{3/2}$) that of a single coil, nearly uniform over a wide region.

∎

Exercise 38.7: Magnetic field of a coaxial cable

Solution

The current flows in the axial direction and the system has rotational symmetry about the axis, so the magnetic field points tangentially to circles centered on the axis, and its magnitude is determined only by the distance $r$ from the axis. Taking as the path a circle of radius $r$ centered on the axis, we have $\oint_C\bm H\cdot\dd\bm\ell=2\pi r\,H(r)=I_{\text{enc}}$. We find $I_{\text{enc}}$ for each region.

(i) $r\lt a$: The current in the inner conductor is uniform over its cross section, so the current passing through a disk of radius $r$ is $I_{\text{enc}}=I\dfrac{\pi r^2}{\pi a^2}=I\dfrac{r^2}{a^2}$, and therefore $H=\dfrac{Ir}{2\pi a^2}$.

(ii) $a\lt r\lt b$: The path encloses only the entire current $I$ of the inner conductor, so $H=\dfrac{I}{2\pi r}$.

(iii) $b\lt r\lt c$: In addition to the current $I$ of the inner conductor, the path encloses the part of the outer conductor (current $-I$, uniform over the cross-sectional area $\pi(c^2-b^2)$) that lies inside radius $r$, whose current is $-I\dfrac{\pi(r^2-b^2)}{\pi(c^2-b^2)}$. Therefore,

$$ I_{\text{enc}}=I-I\frac{r^2-b^2}{c^2-b^2}=I\frac{c^2-r^2}{c^2-b^2}\qquad\therefore\ H=\frac{I}{2\pi r}\cdot\frac{c^2-r^2}{c^2-b^2} $$

(iv) $r\gt c$: The path encloses the entire current $I$ of the inner conductor and the entire current $-I$ of the outer conductor, so $I_{\text{enc}}=I-I=0$, and therefore $H=0$.

Continuity at each boundary can be verified from the fact that at $r=a$ both (i) and (ii) give $\dfrac{I}{2\pi a}$, at $r=b$ both (ii) and (iii) give $\dfrac{I}{2\pi b}$, and at $r=c$ both (iii) and (iv) give $0$. The numerical values are as follows.

$r=0.50\ \mathrm{mm}$: $H=\dfrac{5.0\times0.50\times10^{-3}}{2\pi\times(1.0\times10^{-3})^2}\approx3.98\times10^{2}\ \mathrm{A/m}$. $r=2.0\ \mathrm{mm}$: $H=\dfrac{5.0}{2\pi\times2.0\times10^{-3}}\approx3.98\times10^{2}\ \mathrm{A/m}$. $r=3.5\ \mathrm{mm}$: $I_{\text{enc}}=5.0\times\dfrac{16-12.25}{16-9}\approx2.68\ \mathrm A$, $H=\dfrac{2.68}{2\pi\times3.5\times10^{-3}}\approx1.22\times10^{2}\ \mathrm{A/m}$. $r=5.0\ \mathrm{mm}$: $H=0$.

The reason the magnetic field is $0$ outside the cable is that the currents in the inner conductor (the outgoing current) and in the outer conductor (the return current) are equal in magnitude and opposite in direction, so the net current enclosed by any circle outside the cable is $0$. For this reason, a coaxial cable does not leak a magnetic field to the outside and is also not easily affected by external magnetic fields (noise), and it is used for television antenna lines and high-frequency signal lines.

∎

Exercise 38.8: Magnetic moment of the hydrogen atom in the Bohr model

Solution

(1) The time for the electron to go once around the circular orbit is $T=\dfrac{2\pi a_0}{v}=\dfrac{2\pi\times5.29\times10^{-11}}{2.19\times10^{6}}\approx1.52\times10^{-16}\ \mathrm s$. During this time the charge of magnitude $e$ passes a given point on the orbit once, so the magnitude of the current is

$$ I=\frac eT=\frac{ev}{2\pi a_0}=\frac{(1.602\times10^{-19})\times(2.19\times10^{6})}{2\pi\times5.29\times10^{-11}}\approx1.06\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm A $$

This is about $1.06\ \mathrm{mA}$ (since the electron's charge is negative, the direction of the current is opposite to the electron's motion).

(2) By Definition 38.5, $m=I\times\pi a_0^2$. Substituting $I=\dfrac{ev}{2\pi a_0}$ from (1), we obtain

$$ m=\frac{ev}{2\pi a_0}\cdot\pi a_0^2=\frac{e v a_0}{2}=\frac{(1.602\times10^{-19})\times(2.19\times10^{6})\times(5.29\times10^{-11})}{2}\approx9.28\times10^{-24}\ \mathrm{A\,m^2} $$

This is nearly equal to the Bohr magneton $9.27\times10^{-24}\ \mathrm{A\,m^2}$ ($\mathrm{J/T}$) (the difference is about $0.1\ \%$, which is within the range of the significant figures of the given $v$ and $a_0$). We see that the fundamental unit of the magnetic properties of atoms can be estimated from the value obtained by regarding the orbital motion of the electron as a microscopic circular current.

(3) By Formula 38.3, the magnetic field at the center of the circle (the position of the nucleus) is

$$ H=\frac{I}{2a_0}=\frac{1.056\times10^{-3}}{2\times5.29\times10^{-11}}\approx9.98\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{A/m} $$

and the magnetic flux density is $B=\mu_0H\approx(1.2566\times10^{-6})\times(9.98\times10^{6})\approx12.5\ \mathrm T$. This is a very strong magnetic field, exceeding that of the powerful electromagnets used in laboratories (a few T), and it shows that the orbital motion of the electron alone produces such a magnetic field at the position of the nucleus.

∎

← Back to Chapter 38, “Sources of the Magnetic Field — The Biot–Savart Law and Ampère's Law”

Chapter 39 Magnetic Forces on Currents and Charged Particles — The Lorentz Force and the Hall Effect

Exercise 39.1: Circular motion of an electron in a magnetic field

Solution

The formulas for the radius and period of the circular motion of a charged particle that enters perpendicular to a uniform magnetic field (Formula 39.2 of Chapter 39) are

$$ r=\frac{mv}{qB}, \qquad T=\frac{2\pi m}{qB} $$

We substitute the electron mass $m_{\mathrm e}=9.109\times10^{-31}\ \mathrm{kg}$, the magnitude of its charge $e=1.602\times10^{-19}\ \mathrm C$, the speed $v=3.0\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{m/s}$, and the magnetic flux density $B=1.0\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm T$.

The radius is

$$ r=\frac{m_{\mathrm e}v}{eB}=\frac{9.109\times10^{-31}\times3.0\times10^{6}}{1.602\times10^{-19}\times1.0\times10^{-3}}\ \mathrm m=\frac{2.733\times10^{-24}}{1.602\times10^{-22}}\ \mathrm m\approx1.71\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm m\approx1.7\ \mathrm{cm} $$

The period is

$$ T=\frac{2\pi m_{\mathrm e}}{eB}=\frac{2\pi\times9.109\times10^{-31}}{1.602\times10^{-19}\times1.0\times10^{-3}}\ \mathrm s\approx3.57\times10^{-8}\ \mathrm s\approx36\ \mathrm{ns} $$

The mass of a proton is about 1800 times that of an electron (compare with the proton mass $1.673\times10^{-27}\ \mathrm{kg}$ in Example 39.5), so if a proton circled at the same speed in the same magnetic flux density, both its radius and its period would be about 1800 times larger. We see that, being light, the electron revolves with a small radius and a short period.

Exercise 39.2: Agreement between Fleming's left-hand rule and the cross product

Solution

We take rightward as the positive direction of the $x$ axis, upward as the positive direction of the $y$ axis, and out of the page as the positive direction of the $z$ axis (a right-handed system, $\bm e_x\times\bm e_y=\bm e_z$). As in the hint, we take the direction of the current (the direction of the wire) as the positive $x$ direction and the magnetic field as the positive $y$ direction: $\bm\ell=\ell\,\bm e_x$, $\bm B=B\,\bm e_y$.

By the component calculation of the cross product (University Mathematics, Section 15.4) and $\bm e_x\times\bm e_y=\bm e_z$,

$$ \bm F=I\bm\ell\times\bm B=I\ell B\,(\bm e_x\times\bm e_y)=I\ell B\,\bm e_z $$

That is, the force points in the $+z$ direction (out of the page) and has magnitude $I\ell B$.

Next, we check this with Fleming's left-hand rule. Spread the thumb, index finger, and middle finger of the left hand at right angles to one another, and align the middle finger with the direction of the current ($+x$) and the index finger with the direction of the magnetic field ($+y$). The three fingers of the left hand then form a left-handed system. Let $\bm p$ be the direction of the thumb, $\bm q$ that of the index finger, and $\bm r$ that of the middle finger. For a right-handed system we would have $\bm p\times\bm q=\bm r$, but for a left-handed system $\bm p\times\bm q=-\bm r$. Here $\bm q=\bm e_y,\ \bm r=\bm e_x$, so the thumb must satisfy $\bm p\times\bm e_y=-\bm e_x$. Of the two candidates $\bm p=\pm\bm e_z$, the answer is $\bm p=+\bm e_z$, for which $\bm e_z\times\bm e_y=-\bm e_x$ holds, so the thumb points in the $+z$ direction. This agrees with the result of the cross product, $\bm F\parallel+\bm e_z$.

Let us also check another configuration. When the current points in the $+x$ direction and the magnetic field points into the page (the $-z$ direction, $\bm B=-B\bm e_z$), then since $\bm e_x\times\bm e_z=-\bm e_y$,

$$ \bm F=I\ell\,\bm e_x\times(-B\bm e_z)=-I\ell B\,(\bm e_x\times\bm e_z)=+I\ell B\,\bm e_y $$

so the force points upward. With the left-hand rule, $\bm q=-\bm e_z,\ \bm r=\bm e_x$ gives $\bm p\times(-\bm e_z)=-\bm e_x$, that is, $\bm p\times\bm e_z=\bm e_x$. The vector $\bm p$ satisfying this is the direction of the thumb, and since $\bm e_y\times\bm e_z=\bm e_x$ we get $\bm p=+\bm e_y$ (upward), which again agrees with the result of the cross product.

In fact, in general, when $\bm\ell\perp\bm B$ the force $\bm F=I\bm\ell\times\bm B$ satisfies $\bm F\times\bm B=-B^2\,I\bm\ell$ (since $(\bm\ell\times\bm B)\times\bm B=-\ell B^2\,\bm e_\ell$, where $\bm e_\ell$ is the unit vector in the direction of $\bm\ell$), so the triple $(\bm F,\bm B,\bm\ell)$ always stands in the same relationship as the (thumb, index finger, middle finger) of the left hand. Fleming's left-hand rule is a geometric device devised so that the direction of the force can be found from the shape of the fingers alone, without knowing the operation called the cross product, and it essentially expresses the same physical law.

Exercise 39.3: $\bm E\times\bm B$ drift motion (Advanced)

Solution

When a charged particle (charge $q$, mass $m$) is acted on by forces from both an electric field $\bm E$ and a magnetic field $\bm B$ (perpendicular to each other), its equation of motion is

$$ m\frac{\dd\bm v}{\dd t}=q\bm E+q\bm v\times\bm B $$

We choose coordinates such that $\bm B=B\bm e_z$ and $\bm E=E\bm e_x$ ($\bm E\perp\bm B$). We compute the drift velocity $\bm v_{\mathrm d}=\bm E\times\bm B/B^2$ in components. Since $\bm e_x\times\bm e_z=-\bm e_y$,

$$ \bm E\times\bm B=EB\,(\bm e_x\times\bm e_z)=-EB\,\bm e_y,\qquad \bm v_{\mathrm d}=\frac{-EB\,\bm e_y}{B^2}=-\frac{E}{B}\,\bm e_y $$

Next, to find the Lorentz force that $\bm v_{\mathrm d}$ experiences from the magnetic field, we compute $\bm v_{\mathrm d}\times\bm B$; since $\bm e_y\times\bm e_z=\bm e_x$,

$$ \bm v_{\mathrm d}\times\bm B=-\frac{E}{B}\,B\,(\bm e_y\times\bm e_z)=-E\,\bm e_x=-\bm E $$

This is what we obtain (the same result holds for $\bm E\perp\bm B$ in a general orientation: using the vector triple product formula $\bm a\times(\bm b\times\bm c)=\bm b(\bm a\cdot\bm c)-\bm c(\bm a\cdot\bm b)$, we have $(\bm E\times\bm B)\times\bm B=-\bm B\times(\bm E\times\bm B)=-[\bm E(\bm B\cdot\bm B)-\bm B(\bm B\cdot\bm E)]=-B^2\bm E$ (we used $\bm B\cdot\bm E=0$), so that $\bm v_{\mathrm d}\times\bm B=-\bm E$). Therefore,

$$ q\bm E+q\bm v_{\mathrm d}\times\bm B=q\bm E-q\bm E=\bm 0 $$

Thus, for the component moving with the drift velocity $\bm v_{\mathrm d}$, the Coulomb force due to the electric field and the Lorentz force that this motion itself experiences from the magnetic field exactly cancel, and no net force acts. This does not depend on the sign of $q$ or on $m$.

We therefore split the velocity as $\bm v=\bm v_{\mathrm d}+\bm v'$ and substitute it into the equation of motion. Since $\bm v_{\mathrm d}$ is constant, $\dd\bm v_{\mathrm d}/\dd t=\bm 0$, and

$$ m\frac{\dd\bm v'}{\dd t}=q\bm E+q(\bm v_{\mathrm d}+\bm v')\times\bm B=\bigl(q\bm E+q\bm v_{\mathrm d}\times\bm B\bigr)+q\bm v'\times\bm B=q\bm v'\times\bm B $$

This is the equation of the circular motion (cyclotron motion) due to the Lorentz force that we learned in this chapter, in the same form as in the absence of an electric field.

Therefore, the motion of the charged particle is a combination of “a straight drift at the constant velocity $\bm v_{\mathrm d}=\bm E\times\bm B/B^2$” and “an ordinary circular motion superimposed on it.” When only an electric field is present, the particle keeps being acted on by a force and its speed increases steadily, but when a magnetic field is present at the same time, the part due to the electric force is transformed into a “sideways flow (drift)” at a constant velocity, whose speed does not change. This is why, under crossed electric and magnetic fields, a particle does not keep being accelerated but instead flows along at a constant velocity.

Exercise 39.4: The magnetic mirror (Advanced)

Solution

Consider a charged particle moving slowly, in a helical motion, through a region where the strength of the magnetic field varies from place to place (we assume that the magnetic field changes so gradually that its change during one revolution of the particle is almost negligible). Let the velocity component perpendicular to the magnetic field be $v_\perp=v\sin\theta$ and the parallel component be $v_\parallel=v\cos\theta$. Then the radius of the circular motion is $r=mv_\perp/(qB)$, and the magnetic flux through the surface enclosed by the circle traced out in one revolution is

$$ \Phi=\pi r^2B=\pi\left(\frac{mv_\perp}{qB}\right)^2B=\frac{\pi m^2}{q^2}\cdot\frac{v_\perp^2}{B} $$

When the magnetic field changes slowly, the magnetic flux $\Phi$ enclosed by the circle that the particle goes around is kept approximately constant. This is a fact known as the adiabatic invariant; it can be understood as follows: when the magnetic field changes, electromagnetic induction (Chapter 40) produces an electric field along the circular orbit, which changes the speed of the particle and keeps the magnetic flux nearly constant. This is an advanced topic beyond the scope of this chapter, so here we accept it as a fact and use it. Since $\pi m^2/q^2$ is a constant, $\Phi$ being constant means that $v_\perp^2/B$ is constant, that is,

$$ \frac{v_\perp^2}{B}=\text{constant}\qquad\left(\text{equivalently, the magnetic moment}\ \mu=\frac{mv_\perp^2}{2B}\ \text{is constant}\right) $$

Therefore, as the particle enters a region where the magnetic field $B$ becomes larger, the perpendicular component of its speed increases according to $v_\perp^2\propto B$. On the other hand, since the Lorentz force does no work (Section 39.6), the speed $v=\sqrt{v_\perp^2+v_\parallel^2}$ of the particle stays constant throughout the motion. Since $v_\perp$ increases while $v$ as a whole does not change, the component parallel to the magnetic field, $v_\parallel^2=v^2-v_\perp^2$, decreases by the corresponding amount.

As the particle moves into regions of ever stronger magnetic field, $v_\perp$ keeps increasing and $v_\parallel$ keeps decreasing. When it reaches a sufficiently strong field, we finally have $v_\parallel=0$ (the instant at which the particle no longer advances at all along the direction of the field). This point is called the mirror point. After $v_\parallel=0$, $v_\parallel$ changes sign and becomes negative, and the particle is pushed back in the direction from which it came. This closely resembles a ball climbing a slope: at the point where it has used up its kinetic energy its velocity becomes zero, and it rolls back down the slope and returns—the variation in the strength of the magnetic field plays a role like that of the “slope.” This phenomenon is called the magnetic mirror effect. It is at work in the phenomenon in which charged particles approaching the polar regions (where the magnetic field is strong) along the Earth's magnetic field lines are reflected and confined in the magnetosphere (the Van Allen belts), and it is used as the principle of magnetic confinement devices for nuclear fusion (mirror machines).

However, a particle with a large $v_\parallel$ (a particle whose angle $\theta$ with the magnetic field is small), even though $v_\perp^2/B$ is constant, still has $v_\parallel\gt0$ when it reaches the maximum magnetic field near the pole, and so it is not reflected but precipitates into the atmosphere (the loss cone). It is the particles that precipitate in this way that make the aurora of Section 39.7 glow.

Exercise 39.5: Measuring the carrier density of a semiconductor by the Hall effect

Solution

The expression for the Hall voltage derived in Chapter 39 is

$$ V_{\mathrm H}=\frac{IB}{nqt} $$

Solving this for the carrier density $n$ gives

$$ n=\frac{IB}{qV_{\mathrm H}t} $$

Since the carriers are electrons (n-type), $q=-e$ and $V_{\mathrm H}$ is also negative, but here we use the magnitudes $|q|=e=1.602\times10^{-19}\ \mathrm C$ and $|V_{\mathrm H}|=1.0\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm V$. Substituting the values $I=2.0\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm A$, $B=0.80\ \mathrm T$, and $t=0.50\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm m$ gives

$$ n=\frac{(2.0\times10^{-3})\times0.80}{(1.602\times10^{-19})\times(1.0\times10^{-3})\times(0.50\times10^{-3})}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}} $$

The numerator is $1.6\times10^{-3}$ and the denominator is $1.602\times10^{-19}\times1.0\times10^{-3}\times0.50\times10^{-3}=8.01\times10^{-26}$, so

$$ n\approx\frac{1.6\times10^{-3}}{8.01\times10^{-26}}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}\approx2.0\times10^{22}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}} $$

This is smaller by about six orders of magnitude than the free-electron density of a typical metal ($\sim10^{28}$–$10^{29}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$), and about 20 times the value for the semiconductor treated in Example 39.10 ($n=1.0\times10^{21}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$). It is the value for a rather heavily doped n-type semiconductor, close to the upper end of the carrier densities of doped semiconductors ($10^{20}$–$10^{22}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$) described in the Application box of Chapter 39.

Exercise 39.6: Are the Lorentz force and the Ampère force “the same thing”?—Explain in your own words

Solution

Sample answer (about 200 characters in Japanese):

The Ampère force is a force acting on the whole visible object, the wire, but its true nature is the Lorentz force that each individual free electron moving inside the wire experiences from the magnetic field. Even though the force on each electron is tiny, the wire contains more than $10^{22}$ electrons, and all of them are acted on by forces in the same direction, so the forces simply add up to a force of visible size. A single microscopic law, accumulated, appears as a macroscopic law.

(Key points of the solution: as long as your answer touches on three points—that each individual Lorentz force is tiny, that the number of free electrons is enormous, and that the forces can simply be added because their directions are aligned—you may rephrase it in your own words.)

Exercise 39.7: Identifying the carriers from the sign of the Hall voltage

Solution

(a) The Hall voltage was defined as “the potential of the $-y$ face minus the potential of the $+y$ face.” $V_{\mathrm H}=-25\ \mathrm{mV}\lt0$ means that the potential of the $-y$ face is lower than that of the $+y$ face, that is, that the $-y$ face is charged negatively. When the current is in the $+x$ direction and the magnetic field is in the $+z$ direction, the carriers are pushed toward the $-y$ side and accumulate there whether they are holes or electrons (derivation (1) in Section 39.8), so the charge of the carriers accumulated on the $-y$ face is negative. Therefore the carriers are electrons, and this semiconductor is n-type (Table 39.1).

(b) The charge of the carriers is $q=-e$, and solving the equation $V_{\mathrm H}=IB/(nqt)$ for $n$ gives, in magnitude,

$$ n=\frac{IB}{e\,|V_{\mathrm H}|\,t}=\frac{5.0\times10^{-3}\times0.10}{1.602\times10^{-19}\times25\times10^{-3}\times0.20\times10^{-3}}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}=\frac{5.0\times10^{-4}}{8.01\times10^{-25}}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}\approx6.2\times10^{20}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}} $$

The Hall coefficient, with its sign, is

$$ R_{\mathrm H}=\frac{1}{nq}=-\frac{1}{ne}=-\frac{1}{6.24\times10^{20}\times1.602\times10^{-19}}\ \mathrm{m^3/C}\approx-1.0\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{m^3/C} $$

As a check, $V_{\mathrm H}=R_{\mathrm H}IB/t=-1.0\times10^{-2}\times5.0\times10^{-3}\times0.10/(0.20\times10^{-3})\ \mathrm V=-25\ \mathrm{mV}$, which agrees with the measured value.

Exercise 39.8: The old definition of the ampere and the present $\mu_0$

Solution

(a) Solving the equation $F/\ell=\mu_0I_1I_2/(2\pi a)$ for $\mu_0$ gives $\mu_0=2\pi a\,(F/\ell)/(I_1I_2)$. Substituting the values of the old definition, $I_1=I_2=1\ \mathrm A,\ a=1\ \mathrm m,\ F/\ell=2\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{N/m}$, we get

$$ \mu_0=\frac{2\pi\times1\ \mathrm m\times2\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{N/m}}{1\ \mathrm A\times1\ \mathrm A}=4\pi\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{N/A^2} $$

(The unit is $\mathrm{m\cdot(N/m)/A^2}=\mathrm{N/A^2}$.)

(b) Since $4\pi\times10^{-7}=1.256\,637\,061\,44\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{N/A^2}$, the relative deviation is

$$ \frac{\mu_0-4\pi\times10^{-7}}{4\pi\times10^{-7}}=\frac{(1.256\,637\,062\,12-1.256\,637\,061\,44)\times10^{-6}}{1.256\,637\,061\,44\times10^{-6}}\approx\frac{6.8\times10^{-10}}{1.2566}\approx5.4\times10^{-10} $$

This is larger than the relative uncertainty of the measured value (about $1.5\times10^{-10}$), but extremely small.

(c) Since $F\propto\mu_0$, the force also becomes larger by a relative amount of about $5.4\times10^{-10}$, giving $2\times10^{-7}\times(1+5.4\times10^{-10})\ \mathrm{N/m}\approx2.000\,000\,001\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{N/m}$. To nine significant figures it remains $2\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{N/m}$, so there is practically no difference between the old definition and the present value.

Chapter 40 Electromagnetic Induction and Inductance

Exercise 40.1: Passing a magnet through a coil

Solution

Since the magnet is moved from left to right with its N pole in front, we consider three stages according to the position of the magnet.

(a) While the magnet is approaching the coil. The magnetic field lines emerging from the N pole pass through the coil toward the right, and this flux increases as the magnet approaches. By Lenz's law, the current flows in the direction that produces a leftward magnetic field canceling the increase of the rightward flux. By the right-hand rule, this is counterclockwise as seen from the side of the magnet (the left side) (Table 40.1, (i)).

(b) When the magnet is at the center of the coil. The rightward flux through the coil is at its maximum, and its rate of change $\dd\Phi/\dd t$ is $0$ (the instant at which it changes from increasing to decreasing). By Faraday's law $V=-N\,\dd\Phi/\dd t$, the induced electromotive force (emf) is $0$ at this moment, and the induced current is also $0$. The current reverses its direction from counterclockwise to clockwise, passing through $0$.

(c) While the magnet passes through and moves away. The rear end of the magnet (the S pole side) is leaving the coil. At this time the flux through the coil is still rightward (the field lines outside the magnet go out from the N pole and enter the S pole, so to the left of the S pole they point rightward, toward the magnet), but it decreases as the magnet moves away. By Lenz's law, the current flows in the direction that produces a rightward magnetic field to make up for the decrease of the rightward flux, and this is clockwise as seen from the side of the magnet (the same as (ii) in Table 40.1).

To summarize: when the magnet approaches, the current is counterclockwise; when it passes through and moves away, it is clockwise; and at the center of the coil the current is $0$. The way the flux $\Phi(t)$ changes as “increase → maximum → decrease” (the change of sign of $\dd\Phi/\dd t$) appears directly as the reversal of the direction of the current.

Exercise 40.2: Comparing $\operatorname{rot}\bm E=-\partial\bm B/\partial t$ and $\operatorname{div}\bm D=\rho$

Solution

Model answer (about 200 characters in the Japanese original). The divergence $\operatorname{div}$ is an operator that measures outflow (sources), and the curl $\operatorname{rot}$ is an operator that measures the strength of vortices;$\operatorname{div}\bm D=\rho$ expresses that electric charges are the sources of the electric field, and $\operatorname{rot}\bm E=-\partial\bm B/\partial t$ expresses that the electric field does net work along a closed curve (it circulates in vortices). On the right-hand side, whereas the former has a source, the charge density, the latter has no intrinsic source corresponding to charge, and instead the time variation of the magnetic field appears. The source of the vortices of the electric field is not a new kind of charge but the time variation of the magnetic field.

Supplement. In a vacuum $\bm D=\varepsilon_0\bm E$, so $\operatorname{div}\bm D=\rho$ has the same content as $\operatorname{div}\bm E=\rho/\varepsilon_0$. Also, if magnetic monopoles (magnetic charges) existed, a term corresponding to the flow of magnetic charge would have to be added to the right-hand side of the equation for $\operatorname{rot}\bm E$ as well, but no such thing has been observed. In an electrostatic field $\operatorname{rot}\bm E=\bm 0$, so vortices of the electric field appear only when the magnetic field varies in time.

Exercise 40.3: Dimensional analysis of the electromotive force of a moving conducting rod

Solution

From the Lorentz force expression $F=qvB$ we have $B=F/(qv)$, and using $q=It$ (charge = current × time), the unit of magnetic flux density is

$$ 1\ \mathrm T=\frac{\mathrm N}{\mathrm C\cdot\mathrm{m/s}}=\frac{\mathrm N}{\mathrm A\,\mathrm s\cdot\mathrm{m/s}}=\frac{\mathrm N}{\mathrm A\,\mathrm m} $$

Since $1\ \mathrm N=\mathrm{kg\,m/s^2}$,

$$ 1\ \mathrm T=\frac{\mathrm{kg\,m\,s^{-2}}}{\mathrm A\,\mathrm m}=\mathrm{kg\,s^{-2}\,A^{-1}} $$

Therefore, since $\ell$ is in $\mathrm m$ and $v$ is in $\mathrm{m/s}$, the unit of $B\ell v$ is

$$ [B][\ell][v]=(\mathrm{kg\,s^{-2}\,A^{-1}})\cdot\mathrm m\cdot(\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}})=\mathrm{kg\,m^2\,s^{-3}\,A^{-1}} $$

On the other hand, the unit of voltage, $\mathrm V$, is “power ÷ current”; the unit of power, $\mathrm W$, is “work ÷ time”; and the unit of work (energy), $\mathrm J$, is $\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s^2}$ (from the unit of the kinetic energy $\frac12mv^2$). Hence

$$ 1\ \mathrm V=\frac{1\ \mathrm W}{1\ \mathrm A}=\frac{1\ \mathrm{J/s}}{1\ \mathrm A}=\frac{\mathrm{kg\,m^2\,s^{-2}}}{\mathrm s\cdot\mathrm A}=\mathrm{kg\,m^2\,s^{-3}\,A^{-1}} $$

which is exactly the same as the unit of $B\ell v$. Therefore the right-hand side of $V=B\ell v$ indeed has the unit of voltage (this check starts only from the Lorentz force and the definition of electric power, without relying on the unit in Faraday's law, $\mathrm{Wb}=\mathrm{V\,s}$, so it is an independent check).

Exercise 40.4: Time constant and energy of an RL circuit

Solution

(1) In the steady state the current in the coil is no longer changing ($\dd I/\dd t=0$), so the voltage drop across the coil is zero, and the entire emf of the battery appears across the resistor. By Ohm's law,

$$ I_\infty=\frac{V_0}{R}=\frac{20}{100}=0.20\ \mathrm A $$

(2) Using the formula $U_L=\frac12LI^2$ of the main text, the energy stored in the coil is

$$ U_L=\frac12LI_\infty^2=\frac12\times0.50\times0.20^2=\frac12\times0.50\times0.040=0.010\ \mathrm J $$

(3) After the battery is disconnected, the decay of the current follows the equation of the main text ($I(t)=I_0\exp(-t/\tau)$, $\tau=L/R$). Here $I_0=I_\infty=0.20\ \mathrm A$, and the time constant is

$$ \tau=\frac{L}{R}=\frac{0.50}{100}=5.0\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm s $$

To find the time $t_{1/2}$ at which the current becomes half of its steady value, $I_0/2$, it suffices to solve $I_0\exp(-t_{1/2}/\tau)=I_0/2$. Dividing both sides by $I_0$ gives $\exp(-t_{1/2}/\tau)=1/2$; taking the natural logarithm of both sides gives $-t_{1/2}/\tau=\ln(1/2)=-\ln2$, and therefore

$$ t_{1/2}=\tau\ln2\approx5.0\times10^{-3}\times0.6931\approx3.47\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm s\approx3.5\ \mathrm{ms} $$

This is the required time.

Exercise 40.5: Changing the period of an LC circuit

Solution

By the formula of the main text, the angular frequency of an LC circuit is $\omega=1/\sqrt{LC}$ and the period is $T=2\pi/\omega=2\pi\sqrt{LC}$. If the capacitance is changed as $C\to4C$ without changing the self-inductance $L$, the new period is

$$ T'=2\pi\sqrt{L\cdot4C}=2\pi\sqrt{4}\sqrt{LC}=2\times2\pi\sqrt{LC}=2T $$

In other words, quadrupling the capacitance doubles the period (and halves the angular frequency). Concretely, for the original period $T=1.0\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm s$, the new period is $T'=2.0\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm s$.

By the equation of the main text, the amplitude of the current is $I_{\max}=Q_0\omega$. Since the initial charge $Q_0$ of the capacitor is unchanged, the amplitude is proportional to $\omega'=\omega/2$ (from $C\to4C$, $\omega=1/\sqrt{LC}\to1/\sqrt{4LC}=\omega/2$), so

$$ \frac{I_{\max}'}{I_{\max}}=\frac{Q_0\omega'}{Q_0\omega}=\frac{\omega'}{\omega}=\frac12 $$

This shows that the amplitude of the current becomes $\dfrac12$ times its original value. We see that when the capacitance is increased (the voltage is lower for the same charge), the oscillation becomes slower and the amplitude of the current also becomes smaller.

Exercise 40.6: Lenz's law and the law of conservation of energy

Solution

Suppose that Lenz's law were reversed—that is, that the induced current flowed in the direction that reinforces the change of the flux. For example, when the N pole of a magnet is brought close to the coil, the flux through the coil is increasing to begin with. If Lenz's law were reversed, the induced current would produce a magnetic field in the direction that pushes this increase still further. Such a coil (an electromagnet) would exert on the magnet a force in the direction that attracts it. Then the magnet would be drawn toward the coil and accelerate on its own, without any external force; the closer it got to the coil, the larger the rate of change of the flux would become, the stronger the induced current would become, and the stronger the attractive force would become—both the kinetic energy of the magnet and the Joule heat that the induced current generates in the resistance would keep increasing without limit, even though no energy is supplied from outside. This clearly violates the law of conservation of energy and is physically impossible. Therefore the induced current must be in the direction that cancels the change of the flux (the direction in which it exerts a force that pushes the magnet away)—this is the reason for the direction in Lenz's law.

The same holds for electromagnetic braking (eddy-current braking). When a conductor moves in a magnetic field, it functions as a “brake” precisely because the force produced by the eddy currents always acts in the direction that opposes the motion of the conductor. If the direction of the force were reversed—the direction that reinforces the motion—the conductor, instead of being braked, would keep accelerating on its own although no energy is supplied from outside, which would also violate the law of conservation of energy. The very fact that electromagnetic brakes observed in practice always act as braking (deceleration) is one piece of evidence that the direction given by Lenz's law is physically correct.

Exercise 40.7: Maximum and effective values of a generator's electromotive force

Solution

(1) The angular velocity is $\omega=2\pi f=2\pi\times60\approx376.99\ \mathrm{rad/s}$. By Formula 40.6, the maximum value (amplitude) is

$$ V_0=NBA\omega=50\times0.30\times0.040\times376.99\ \mathrm V=0.60\times376.99\ \mathrm V\approx226.2\ \mathrm V\approx2.3\times10^{2}\ \mathrm V $$

By Formula 40.7, the effective value is

$$ V_{\mathrm{eff}}=\frac{V_0}{\sqrt2}=\frac{226.2}{1.4142}\ \mathrm V\approx159.94\ \mathrm V\approx1.6\times10^{2}\ \mathrm V $$

(2) For the same coil and magnetic field, $V_0=NBA\cdot2\pi f$ is proportional to the frequency $f$, and $V_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is also proportional to $f$. To make the effective value $100\ \mathrm V$, the frequency should be multiplied by $100/159.94\approx0.6252$, so

$$ f'=60\times\frac{100}{159.94}\ \mathrm{Hz}\approx37.5\ \mathrm{Hz} $$

(To find it directly, solve $V_{\mathrm{eff}}=NBA\cdot2\pi f/\sqrt2=100\ \mathrm V$ for $f$: $f=\dfrac{100\sqrt2}{2\pi\times50\times0.30\times0.040}\ \mathrm{Hz}\approx37.5\ \mathrm{Hz}$.)

Exercise 40.8: Why is the force of an eddy-current brake proportional to speed?

Solution

(1) The free charges in the plate experience a force $\bm v\times\bm B$ per unit charge (the Lorentz force of Section 40.4). Since $\bm v\perp\bm B$, its magnitude is $vB$. In Ohm's law $\bm j=\sigma\bm E_{\mathrm{eff}}$, if we use this force in place of the electric field, the current density is

$$ \bm j=\sigma\,\bm v\times\bm B,\qquad j=\sigma vB $$

The unit is $\mathrm{S/m}\cdot\mathrm{m/s}\cdot\mathrm T=\mathrm{A/V\cdot(1/s)\cdot V\,s/m^2}=\mathrm{A/m^2}$, which agrees with the unit of current density.

(2) The magnetic force per unit volume (the density of the Ampère force) is $\bm f=\bm j\times\bm B$. For $\bm v\perp\bm B$, using the relation $(\bm v\times\bm B)\times\bm B=-B^2\bm v$ (from $\bm a\times(\bm b\times\bm c)=\bm b(\bm a\cdot\bm c)-\bm c(\bm a\cdot\bm b)$ we get $(\bm v\times\bm B)\times\bm B=-\bm B\times(\bm v\times\bm B)=-[\bm v(\bm B\cdot\bm B)-\bm B(\bm B\cdot\bm v)]=-B^2\bm v$), we obtain

$$ \bm f=\sigma(\bm v\times\bm B)\times\bm B=-\sigma B^2\,\bm v $$

Its magnitude is $f=\sigma B^2v$, which is proportional to the speed $v$, and its direction is opposite to the velocity (the direction that opposes the motion). This means that the proportionality constant $\gamma$ in $F=\gamma v$ of Example 40.1 is a quantity proportional to $\sigma B^2$. The chain of causation—“the greater the speed, the greater the induced emf ($vB$), the greater the current, and the greater the force that this current experiences from the magnetic field”—produces a braking force proportional to the first power of $v$.

(3) Substituting the numerical values,

$$ f=\sigma B^2v=3.5\times10^{7}\times0.50^2\times2.0\ \mathrm{N/m^3}=1.75\times10^{7}\ \mathrm{N/m^3} $$

The magnitude of the force acting on a portion of volume $1.0\ \mathrm{cm^3}=1.0\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{m^3}$ is

$$ F=f\times1.0\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{m^3}=17.5\ \mathrm N\approx18\ \mathrm N $$

Also, the current density is $j=\sigma vB=3.5\times10^{7}\times2.0\times0.50\ \mathrm{A/m^2}=3.5\times10^{7}\ \mathrm{A/m^2}$, and the power dissipated as Joule heat in this portion is

$$ \frac{j^2}{\sigma}\times1.0\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{m^3}=\frac{(3.5\times10^{7})^2}{3.5\times10^{7}}\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm W=35\ \mathrm W $$

This agrees with the power of the braking force,$Fv=17.5\times2.0\ \mathrm W=35\ \mathrm W$, which confirms that the kinetic energy is entirely converted into Joule heat. Note that this is a simple model that ignores such matters as the need for the current to close over the whole plate, so it serves as a guide for estimating an upper bound on the actual value.

← Back to Chapter 40, “Electromagnetic Induction and Inductance”

Chapter 41 Electric and Magnetic Fields in Matter — Dielectrics and Magnetic Materials

Exercise 41.1: Energy change of a capacitor with a dielectric inserted

Solution

(a) Case where the charge $Q$ is held constant (the battery is disconnected before the dielectric is inserted). Let $C_0$ be the capacitance before insertion and $C=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}C_0$ the capacitance after insertion (Law 41.3 of Chapter 41). Since the charge $Q$ does not change, using the electrostatic energy $U=Q^2/(2C)$ gives

$$ U_i=\frac{Q^2}{2C_0},\qquad U_f=\frac{Q^2}{2C}=\frac{Q^2}{2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}C_0}=\frac{U_i}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}} $$

Thus, if $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}\gt1$, then $U_f\lt U_i$; that is, the energy decreases. Since there is no battery, the entire change in the capacitor's energy equals the work done by the hand (the external force), so

$$ W_{\text{hand}}=U_f-U_i=-\Bigl(1-\frac1{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}}\Bigr)U_i\lt0 $$

That the work done by the hand is negative means that the dielectric experiences a force in the direction of being pulled in between the electrodes, so the hand actually has to hold the dielectric back (do negative work) to keep it from rushing in suddenly. Numerical example: for $C_0=88.5\ \mathrm{pF}$, $V=100\ \mathrm V$ ($Q=C_0V=8.85\ \mathrm{nC}$), and $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}=5$, we have $U_i=\frac12C_0V^2=4.43\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm J$, $U_f=U_i/5=8.85\times10^{-8}\ \mathrm J$, and $W_{\text{hand}}=-3.54\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm J$.

(b) Case where the voltage $V$ is held constant (the dielectric is inserted with the battery still connected). Since the voltage $V$ does not change, using $U=\frac12CV^2$ gives

$$ U_i=\frac12C_0V^2,\qquad U_f=\frac12CV^2=\frac12\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}C_0V^2=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}U_i $$

Here the energy stored in the capacitor instead increases: $\Delta U_{\text{cap}}=U_f-U_i=\frac12(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}-1)C_0V^2$. In this case the battery sends an additional charge $\Delta Q=(C-C_0)V=(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}-1)C_0V$ into the capacitor, so the battery does work $W_{\text{battery}}=V\Delta Q=(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}-1)C_0V^2$. This is exactly twice $\Delta U_{\text{cap}}$. From conservation of energy (the increase in the capacitor's energy $=$ the work done by the hand $+$ the work done by the battery),

$$ W_{\text{hand}}=\Delta U_{\text{cap}}-W_{\text{battery}}=\frac12(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}-1)C_0V^2-(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}-1)C_0V^2=-\frac12(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}-1)C_0V^2\lt0 $$

Numerical example: $\Delta U_{\text{cap}}=1.77\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm J$, $W_{\text{battery}}=3.54\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm J$, and $W_{\text{hand}}=-1.77\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm J$. Half of the energy $W_{\text{battery}}$ supplied by the battery becomes the increase in the electrostatic energy of the capacitor, and the other half goes into the work done on the dielectric by the force that pulls it in (which becomes the kinetic energy of the dielectric if the hand does not hold it back, or heat if there is friction).

In both cases $W_{\text{hand}}\lt0$, and the conclusion is the same: “the dielectric is pulled in between the electrodes, and the hand does work on the side of holding it back.” This pulling-in force arises not because the dielectric is attracted to the electrodes, but because the electric field at the edges of the electrodes (the fringe field), which lies near the rims of the electrodes and has a component parallel to the plate surfaces, polarizes the dielectric and pulls it toward the region of stronger field (between the electrodes). Once the dielectric has been fully inserted, there is no such force.

Exercise 41.2: Rederiving the magnetic field of a solenoid with a magnetic material from Ampère's law

Solution

Take a rectangular path $C$ in a plane containing the axis of the solenoid—one side (of length $L$) runs through the interior of the solenoid parallel to the axis, the opposite side runs outside the solenoid (where the field can be regarded as $\bm 0$ because the solenoid is sufficiently long), and the remaining two sides are taken perpendicular to the axis of the solenoid (perpendicular to the interior field $\bm H$). Then the contribution to the line integral $\displaystyle\oint_C\bm H\cdot\dd\bm l$ comes only from the one side passing through the interior (the outer side has $H\approx0$, and the two perpendicular sides are orthogonal to $\bm H$ and so do not contribute). If the interior side has length $L$ and the magnetic field strength there is $H$ (along the axis, assumed constant), then

$$ \oint_C\bm H\cdot\dd\bm l=HL $$

On the other hand, the free current that this path $C$ “picks up” is the free current threading the windings of the solenoid. With $n$ turns per unit length and current $I$ in the wire, the number of turns contained in a section of length $L$ is $nL$, so

$$ I_{\mathrm{f},\text{enc}}=nL\times I=nLI $$

By Ampère's law (integral form) in Law 41.6, $\displaystyle\oint_C\bm H\cdot\dd\bm l=I_{\mathrm{f},\text{enc}}$, we have

$$ HL=nLI\qquad\therefore\quad H=nI $$

This result contains no trace of the permeability $\mu$ of the iron core (or the relative permeability $\mu_{\mathrm{r}}$). This is because Law 41.6 sees only the free current, and the effect of the response of the magnetic material (the magnetization current) has already been absorbed into the definition of $\bm H$ (thanks to the symmetry of an infinitely long solenoid, or of a solenoid long enough that end effects can be neglected, $H$ is determined from the integral form alone). Therefore $H=nI$ does not change whether or not there is an iron core. The magnetic flux density follows from the constitutive equation $\bm B=\mu_0\mu_{\mathrm{r}}\bm H$ of a linear magnetic material (Law 41.4):

$$ B=\mu_0\mu_{\mathrm{r}}H=\mu_0\mu_{\mathrm{r}}nI $$

This agrees completely with the result of Example 41.7 (the same $H,B$ are obtained regardless of how the path length $L$ is chosen).

Exercise 41.3: Differences in the physical meaning of relative permittivity and relative permeability

Solution

(a) In a dielectric, for each of electronic polarization, ionic polarization, and orientational polarization (Section 41.1), the dipoles align parallel to the electric field $\bm E$ (the induced dipole is parallel to $\bm E$, in the orientation that minimizes $U=-\bm p\cdot\bm E$). The electric field produced inside a material polarized in this way points from the positive charges to the negative charges, that is, in the direction opposite to $\bm P$ (inside a uniformly polarized flat plate such as that in a capacitor it is $-\bm P/\varepsilon_0$, the depolarizing field of Section 41.1.3). Hence polarization acts in the direction of weakening the field for a fixed free charge, and the static relative permittivity is always $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}\geq1$ (at high frequencies, near a resonance, $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}\lt1$ is possible, but this is not a static property). For magnetic materials, the situation stands in contrast. The magnetic field produced by a magnetic dipole (circular current) points in the same direction as $\bm m$ inside the loop ($\bm B=\mu_0\bm M$ inside a uniformly magnetized cylinder), so when permanent moments align parallel to $\bm H$, as in paramagnetism and ferromagnetism, $\bm B=\mu_0(\bm H+\bm M)$ is strengthened and $\mu_{\mathrm{r}}\gt1$. Moreover, magnetic materials exhibit diamagnetism, which has no counterpart on the electric side. This arises because the magnetic moment induced in the orbital motion of the electrons when a magnetic field is applied appears, by Lenz's law, in the direction that weakens the original field ($\bm M$ opposite to $\bm H$), and this is the reason why $\mu_{\mathrm{r}}\lt1$ (on the electric side, there is no such thing as an induced dipole pointing opposite to $\bm E$). In ferromagnetism, because the exchange interaction (Section 41.2.3) produces an alignment so strong that $\bm M$ is larger than $\bm H$ by orders of magnitude, $\mu_{\mathrm{r}}\gg1$.

(b) A ferroelectric (Section 41.1.4) has a spontaneous polarization $P_{\mathrm{s}}$ even without an external electric field, owing to the asymmetry of its crystal structure, and its $P$–$E$ relation is nonlinear and shows hysteresis (a history-dependent phenomenon in which the polarization does not reverse immediately when the electric field is reversed). A ferromagnet (Section 41.2.4) likewise has spontaneous magnetization in its magnetic domains even without an external magnetic field, and its $M$–$H$ (or $B$–$H$) relation is nonlinear and shows hysteresis (Law 41.5). Both share the two properties of “having spontaneous order (spontaneous polarization, spontaneous magnetization)” and “the response not following immediately when the external field is reversed (history, hysteresis),” and neither can be described by a single constant such as the relative permittivity or the relative permeability. This similarity originates from the fact that in both there is, inside the system, “an interaction by which neighboring units (dipoles, magnetic moments) tend to align in the same direction” (the exchange interaction in a ferromagnet; in a ferroelectric, the interaction within the crystal and its coupling to the displacement of the lattice), and a phase transition to a state with spontaneous order occurs below the Curie temperature.

Exercise 41.4: Deriving $\bm D,\bm E,\bm P$ and $\bm B,\bm H,\bm M$ from one another

Solution

(a) Solving the definition $\bm D=\varepsilon_0\bm E+\bm P$ for $\bm P$ gives

$$ \bm P=\bm D-\varepsilon_0\bm E $$

Solving for $\bm E$ gives

$$ \bm E=\frac{\bm D-\bm P}{\varepsilon_0} $$

(b) Solving the definition $\bm H=\bm B/\mu_0-\bm M$ (that is, $\bm B=\mu_0(\bm H+\bm M)$) for $\bm M$ gives

$$ \bm M=\frac{\bm B}{\mu_0}-\bm H $$

Solving for $\bm H$ gives (the original definition itself)

$$ \bm H=\frac{\bm B}{\mu_0}-\bm M $$

(c) In a linear medium, substituting $\bm P=\varepsilon_0\chi_{\mathrm{e}}\bm E$ into $\bm D=\varepsilon_0\bm E+\bm P$ gives

$$ \bm D=\varepsilon_0\bm E+\varepsilon_0\chi_{\mathrm{e}}\bm E=\varepsilon_0(1+\chi_{\mathrm{e}})\bm E\equiv\varepsilon\bm E,\qquad\varepsilon=\varepsilon_0(1+\chi_{\mathrm{e}}) $$

Similarly, substituting $\bm M=\chi_{\mathrm{m}}\bm H$ into $\bm B=\mu_0(\bm H+\bm M)$ gives

$$ \bm B=\mu_0(\bm H+\chi_{\mathrm{m}}\bm H)=\mu_0(1+\chi_{\mathrm{m}})\bm H\equiv\mu\bm H,\qquad\mu=\mu_0(1+\chi_{\mathrm{m}}) $$

Both relations are thus obtained.

Exercise 41.5: (Advanced) The Meissner effect of superconductors versus ordinary diamagnetism

Solution

(a) Comparing the orders of magnitude of the susceptibility, the susceptibility of ordinary diamagnetic materials such as copper and bismuth is of the order of $\chi_{\mathrm{m}}\sim-10^{-5}$–$-10^{-4}$, and they weaken the external magnetic field only very slightly (see Figure 41.9 in this chapter). In contrast, the Meissner effect of a superconductor makes the magnetic flux density inside completely $\bm B=\bm 0$. From $\bm B=\mu_0(\bm H+\bm M)=\bm 0$, this corresponds to $\bm M=-\bm H$, that is, $\chi_{\mathrm{m}}=-1$, which is larger in magnitude than that of ordinary diamagnetic materials by four to five orders of magnitude ($10^4$–$10^5$ times), a response that is stronger by orders of magnitude.

(b) As seen in Section 41.2.3, ordinary diamagnetism is an effect (analogous to electromagnetic induction) in which the orbital motion of the electrons within individual atoms and molecules is slightly altered by the magnetic field; it is merely a weak induced effect, far from completely excluding the magnetic field. The Meissner effect in a superconductor, on the other hand, is an essentially macroscopic quantum effect that arises when a phase-coherent macroscopic quantum state (a condensate of Cooper pairs) forms throughout the material and a supercurrent reflecting that macroscopic quantum state (a persistent current without decay) flows near the surface of the material. The magnetic field created by this supercurrent exactly cancels the external magnetic field and makes the internal $\bm B$ completely $\bm 0$. That is, whereas ordinary diamagnetism is a microscopic and slight effect, “the orbital motion of electrons within individual atoms,” the Meissner effect is a phenomenon entirely different in origin, “a macroscopic supercurrent threading the entire material” (the detailed mechanism of superconductivity itself is beyond the scope of this book).

(c) Even a “perfect conductor” with electrical resistance $0$ keeps its internal $\bm B$ at $\bm 0$ if it is cooled before being placed in a magnetic field (a state with $\bm B=\bm 0$) and the field is applied afterward: a current is induced (by Lenz's law) in the direction that cancels the change in flux and continues to flow without decay. However, when it is cooled with the field already applied, there is no change in flux in a perfect conductor, so the internal $\bm B$ stays at its value from before the cooling (the flux is trapped). A superconductor, in contrast, even when cooled with the field applied, expels the flux from its interior and makes $\bm B=\bm 0$ once the temperature falls below the critical temperature. That is, the Meissner effect (flux expulsion) cannot be explained by the resistance being $0$ (a perfect conductor) alone; it is a property of the superconducting state itself. Note that the $\bm B=\bm 0$ in this problem refers to a type-I superconductor when the magnetic field is smaller than the critical field. In a type-II superconductor, between the lower critical field and the upper critical field, the flux partially penetrates as thin vortex filaments (quantized flux), and $\chi_{\mathrm{m}}=-1$ does not hold.

Exercise 41.6: Multilayer ceramic capacitors and the dielectric breakdown of air

Solution

(a) The capacitance of one layer is $C_1=\varepsilon_0\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}S/d$, so

$$ C_1=(8.854\times10^{-12}\ \mathrm{F/m})\times3000\times\frac{1.0\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{m^2}}{2.0\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm m}\approx1.33\times10^{-8}\ \mathrm F=13.3\ \mathrm{nF} $$

(b) In a parallel connection the capacitances add, so for 100 layers $C=100\,C_1\approx1.33\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm F=1.33\ \mu\mathrm F$. Even for the same volume, stacking hundreds of thin, high-permittivity layers yields a large capacitance.

(c) $E=V/d=(5\ \mathrm V)/(2.0\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm m)=2.5\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{V/m}$, and $D=\varepsilon_0\varepsilon_{\mathrm{r}}E=(8.854\times10^{-12})(3000)(2.5\times10^{6})\approx6.6\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{C/m^2}$. This $E$ is about $83\%$ of the dielectric breakdown field of air (about $3\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{V/m}$), a strength at which air would be on the verge of discharge, but the breakdown field of ceramics is far higher than that of air, so there is no problem in using it. When the layers are made thinner, the electric field becomes stronger even at the same voltage ($E=V/d$), so the voltage that can be withstood is determined by the thickness of the layers and the breakdown field of the material.

(d) $V=E_{\text{breakdown}}d=(3\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{V/m})(1\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm m)=3\times10^{3}\ \mathrm V=3\ \mathrm{kV}$.

Exercise 41.7: Heating of a toroidal iron core and of a transformer core

Solution

(a) Apply Ampère's law (Law 41.6) to the path going once around inside the core (of length $2\pi r$). The free current threading this path is $NI$ (the winding passes through it $N$ times), so

$$ H\cdot2\pi r=NI\qquad\therefore\quad H=\frac{NI}{2\pi r}=\frac{500\times0.10}{2\pi\times0.050}\ \mathrm{A/m}\approx1.59\times10^{2}\ \mathrm{A/m} $$

The magnetic flux density is $B=\mu_0\mu_{\mathrm{r}}H=(1.2566\times10^{-6})(2000)(159.2)\approx0.40\ \mathrm T$ (without the iron core, $\mu_0H\approx0.20\ \mathrm{mT}$). The magnetization is $M=(\mu_{\mathrm{r}}-1)H=1999\times159.2\approx3.18\times10^{5}\ \mathrm{A/m}$. Check: $\mu_0(H+M)=\mu_0\times3.18\times10^{5}\approx0.40\ \mathrm T=B$.

(b) The hysteresis loop area of $300\ \mathrm{J/m^3}$ is the thermal energy lost per unit volume in one cycle (one repetition of magnetization). The volume of the core is $V=m/\rho=(1.0\ \mathrm{kg})/(7.65\times10^3\ \mathrm{kg/m^3})=1.31\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{m^3}$, and the energy lost in one cycle is $300\times1.31\times10^{-4}\approx3.9\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm J$. At $50\ \mathrm{Hz}$ there are 50 cycles per second, so the heating power is

$$ P=(3.9\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm J)\times50\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}\approx1.96\ \mathrm W\approx2.0\ \mathrm W $$

That is about $2\ \mathrm W$ per $1\ \mathrm{kg}$, and this accumulates into non-negligible heat generation in large transformers. This is the reason for using soft magnetic materials (such as silicon steel), whose hysteresis loops have a small area.

Exercise 41.8: Curie's law for paramagnetic salts and the saturation magnetization of iron

Solution

(a) From $\chi_{\mathrm{m}}=C/T$, $\chi_{\mathrm{m}}(77\ \mathrm K)/\chi_{\mathrm{m}}(300\ \mathrm K)=300/77\approx3.90$. Cooling to the temperature of liquid nitrogen increases the susceptibility by a factor of about four.

(b) $\mu_{\mathrm B}B/(k_{\mathrm B}T)=\dfrac{(9.274\times10^{-24}\ \mathrm{J/T})(1.0\ \mathrm T)}{(1.380649\times10^{-23}\ \mathrm{J/K})(300\ \mathrm K)}\approx2.24\times10^{-3}$. Since this ratio is much smaller than $1$, $\tanh x\approx x$ can be used, giving

$$ M\approx N\mu_{\mathrm B}\cdot\frac{\mu_{\mathrm B}B}{k_{\mathrm B}T}=(5.0\times10^{27})(9.274\times10^{-24})(2.24\times10^{-3})\approx1.04\times10^{2}\ \mathrm{A/m} $$

The susceptibility is, from $B\approx\mu_0H$, $\chi_{\mathrm{m}}=M/H\approx\mu_0M/B=(1.2566\times10^{-6})(1.04\times10^{2})/1.0\approx1.3\times10^{-4}$. This also agrees with $C/T=0.039/300\approx1.3\times10^{-4}$, obtained using the Curie constant $C=\mu_0N\mu_{\mathrm B}^2/k_{\mathrm B}\approx0.039\ \mathrm K$.

(c) The number density of iron atoms is $N=\rho N_{\mathrm A}/M_{\mathrm{mol}}=(7.87\times10^3\ \mathrm{kg/m^3})(6.022\times10^{23}\ \mathrm{mol^{-1}})/(0.05585\ \mathrm{kg/mol})\approx8.49\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$. The saturation magnetization when all the moments are aligned is $M_{\mathrm s}=N\times2.2\mu_{\mathrm B}\approx1.73\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{A/m}$, giving $\mu_0M_{\mathrm s}\approx2.2\ \mathrm T$, which is in good agreement with the saturation magnetic flux density of iron (about $2.1$–$2.2\ \mathrm T$).

Chapter 42 Maxwell's Equations and Electromagnetic Waves

Exercise 42.1: Contradiction in Ampère's law for a circuit containing a capacitor

Solution

(1) The contradiction when the displacement current is not included. Ampère's law without the displacement current is $\oint_C\bm H\cdot\dd\bm\ell=I_{\text{through}}$ ($I_{\text{through}}$ is the total conduction current passing through a surface bounded by $C$). Consider two surfaces that have the same closed curve $C$ (a loop going once around the wire) as their boundary.

  • (a) The flat surface $S_1$ cutting straight across the wire: the current flowing in the wire passes through it directly, so $I_{\text{through}}=I=1.5\ \mathrm A$. Therefore $\oint_C\bm H\cdot\dd\bm\ell=1.5\ \mathrm A$.
  • (b) The surface $S_2$ passing between the plates: there is no wire between the plates and no movement of charge (conduction current), so $I_{\text{through}}=0$. Therefore $\oint_C\bm H\cdot\dd\bm\ell=0$.

Although the left-hand side $\oint_C\bm H\cdot\dd\bm\ell$ is the same, its value comes out as either $1.5\ \mathrm A$ or $0$ depending on the choice of surface, which is a contradiction. For example, for the magnetic field on a circle $C$ at distance $r$ from the wire, using $S_1$ gives $\oint_C\bm H\cdot\dd\bm\ell=I$, which agrees with the actual field $H=I/(2\pi r)$. Using $S_2$, however, makes the right-hand side $0$, which contradicts the actual field (the magnetic field itself does not change with the choice of surface; rather, the evaluation of the right-hand side of the law disagrees between the two surfaces).

(2) When the displacement current is included. Since the capacitor is charged with a constant current $I$, the charge on the plates at time $t$ (with $t=0$ being the instant when the charge is $0$) is $Q=It$. Neglecting edge effects, the electric field between the plates is uniform, with $E=\sigma/\varepsilon_0=Q/(\varepsilon_0A)=It/(\varepsilon_0A)$. Therefore we have

$$ \pdiff Et=\frac{I}{\varepsilon_0A}=\frac{1.5}{(8.854\times10^{-12})(1.0\times10^{-2})}\approx1.69\times10^{13}\ \mathrm{V/(m\cdot s)} $$

The displacement current density is $j_{\mathrm d}=\pdiff Dt=\varepsilon_0\pdiff Et=I/A=1.5/(1.0\times10^{-2})=1.5\times10^2\ \mathrm{A/m^2}$ (the units agree: $\mathrm{F/m}\cdot\mathrm{V/(m\,s)}=\mathrm{C/(m^2\,s)}=\mathrm{A/m^2}$).
