Since the surface $S_2$ covers the whole plate area $A$ and is pierced by the electric field, the displacement current through $S_2$ is

$$ I_{\mathrm d}=j_{\mathrm d}A=1.5\times10^2\times1.0\times10^{-2}=1.5\ \mathrm A $$

Using the Ampère–Maxwell law $\oint_C\bm H\cdot\dd\bm\ell=\iint_S\left(\bm j+\pdiff{\bm D}{t}\right)\cdot\dd\bm S$, we find:

  • $S_1$: conduction current $1.5\ \mathrm A$; since this surface does not cut through the plates, the displacement current can be neglected; total $1.5\ \mathrm A$.
  • $S_2$: conduction current $0$, displacement current $1.5\ \mathrm A$; total $1.5\ \mathrm A$.

Thus, whichever surface is chosen, the right-hand side takes the same value, $1.5\ \mathrm A$, and the contradiction is resolved.

Exercise 42.2: Proof of a vector identity using components

Solution

Let $\bm f=[f,g,h]$. By the definition of the curl,

$$ \rot\bm f=\left[\pdiff hy-\pdiff gz,\ \pdiff fz-\pdiff hx,\ \pdiff gx-\pdiff fy\right] $$

For this $\rot\bm f$, the $y$ component of its curl is obtained by shifting the indices one step cyclically in the definition of the curl ($x\to y\to z\to x$):

$$ \bigl[\rot(\rot\bm f)\bigr]_y=\pdiff{}{z}(\rot\bm f)_x-\pdiff{}{x}(\rot\bm f)_z $$

Substituting $(\rot\bm f)_x,(\rot\bm f)_z$ gives

$$ \bigl[\rot(\rot\bm f)\bigr]_y=\pdiff{}{z}\left(\pdiff hy-\pdiff gz\right)-\pdiff{}{x}\left(\pdiff gx-\pdiff fy\right) =\frac{\partial^2h}{\partial z\partial y}-\frac{\partial^2g}{\partial z^2}-\frac{\partial^2g}{\partial x^2}+\frac{\partial^2f}{\partial x\partial y} $$

The order of partial differentiation can be exchanged (Schwarz's theorem), so we can write $\dfrac{\partial^2h}{\partial z\partial y}=\dfrac{\partial^2h}{\partial y\partial z}$ and $\dfrac{\partial^2f}{\partial x\partial y}=\dfrac{\partial^2f}{\partial y\partial x}$. Now adding and subtracting $\dfrac{\partial^2g}{\partial y^2}$ (that is, adding $0$), we obtain

$$ \bigl[\rot(\rot\bm f)\bigr]_y=\underbrace{\left(\frac{\partial^2f}{\partial y\partial x}+\frac{\partial^2g}{\partial y^2}+\frac{\partial^2h}{\partial y\partial z}\right)}_{=\ \pdiff{}{y}\left(\pdiff fx+\pdiff gy+\pdiff hz\right)} -\underbrace{\left(\frac{\partial^2g}{\partial x^2}+\frac{\partial^2g}{\partial y^2}+\frac{\partial^2g}{\partial z^2}\right)}_{=\ \nabla^2g} $$

The first part is $\pdiff{}{y}(\operatorname{div}\bm f)=\bigl[\grad(\operatorname{div}\bm f)\bigr]_y$, and the second part is $\nabla^2g=\bigl[\nabla^2\bm f\bigr]_y$. Hence

$$ \bigl[\rot(\rot\bm f)\bigr]_y=\bigl[\grad(\operatorname{div}\bm f)\bigr]_y-\bigl[\nabla^2\bm f\bigr]_y $$

holds. The $z$ component can be computed in the same way, and the identity $\rot(\rot\bm f)=\grad(\operatorname{div}\bm f)-\nabla^2\bm f$ has been verified for all three components.

Supplement (checking types). Since the left-hand side $\rot(\rot\bm f)$ is a vector, every term on the right-hand side must also be a vector. $\grad(\operatorname{div}\bm f)$ is “the gradient of the scalar $\operatorname{div}\bm f$,” so it is a vector, and one cannot add $\operatorname{div}\bm f$ itself (a scalar). In the $y$ component, the expression $\pdiff{}{y}(\operatorname{div}\bm f)$ that appeared midway, that is, the operation of “partial differentiation with respect to $y$,” corresponds to the $y$ component of the gradient.

Exercise 42.3: Correspondence between the four Maxwell's equations and the names of the laws

Solution

(1) The names of the laws and their starting points.

  • ① Integral form $\oint_S\bm D\cdot\dd\bm S=Q_{\text{enc}}$: Gauss's law (Gauss's law for the electric field). In Chapter 35 it was derived starting from Coulomb's law, using the divergence theorem.
  • ② Integral form $\oint_S\bm B\cdot\dd\bm S=0$: Gauss's law for the magnetic field (conservation of magnetic flux). In Chapter 38 it was derived using the divergence theorem, from the fact that magnetic field lines have no ends anywhere and always close on themselves.
  • ③ Integral form $\oint_C\bm E\cdot\dd\bm\ell=-\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}\iint_S\bm B\cdot\dd\bm S$: Faraday's law of induction. In Chapter 40 it was derived from the relation between the time variation of magnetic flux and the induced electromotive force (emf), as found in experiments on electromagnetic induction.
  • ④ Integral form $\oint_C\bm H\cdot\dd\bm\ell=\iint_S\left(\bm j+\pdiff{\bm D}{t}\right)\cdot\dd\bm S$: Ampère–Maxwell law. This is Ampère's law of Chapter 38 (an electric current produces a magnetic field), with the displacement current $\pdiff{\bm D}{t}$ added in Section 42.1 of this chapter for consistency with conservation of charge.

(2) Why ② means that “magnetic monopoles do not exist.” Equation ① has the form $\operatorname{div}\bm D=\rho$, and the charge density $\rho$ on the right-hand side represents the sources (positive charge) and sinks (negative charge) of the electric field lines. Objects carrying only positive charge and objects carrying only negative charge actually exist, so there are places where $\rho\neq0$, and electric field lines emerge from them. On the other hand, ② can be written $\operatorname{div}\bm B=0$, so the “magnetic charge density” corresponding to $\rho$ in ① is $0$ everywhere. This means that there are no isolated magnetic poles (magnetic monopoles) to serve as sources or sinks of magnetic field lines, and magnetic field lines always form closed loops (field lines leaving the north pole always enter the south pole). This also agrees with the fact (Example 42.3) that when a bar magnet is broken in half, no piece with only a north pole or only a south pole is produced; each piece becomes a smaller magnet having a north pole and a south pole again. The asymmetry between electricity and magnetism—that electric charge exists as single particles (positive and negative charges) while nothing of the kind has been found for magnetism—appears as the difference between the right-hand sides of ① and ② ($\rho$ versus $0$).

Exercise 42.4: A plane wave with a different propagation direction

Solution

We have $\bm E=[0,E_y,0]$ and $E_y=E_0\cos(kx-\omega t)$, where $E_y$ is a function of $x,t$ only. Computing the curl component by component,

$$ \rot\bm E=\left[\pdiff{E_z}{y}-\pdiff{E_y}{z},\ \pdiff{E_x}{z}-\pdiff{E_z}{x},\ \pdiff{E_y}{x}-\pdiff{E_x}{y}\right]=\left[0,\ 0,\ \pdiff{E_y}{x}\right] $$

($E_x=E_z=0$, and since $E_y$ does not contain $y,z$, $\pdiff{E_y}{z}=0$). Since $\pdiff{E_y}{x}=-kE_0\sin(kx-\omega t)$, we get $\rot\bm E=[0,0,-kE_0\sin(kx-\omega t)]$, and this time only the $z$ component is not $0$. From Faraday's law $\rot\bm E=-\pdiff{\bm B}{t}$, letting $\bm B=[0,0,B_z]$, we have

$$ -\pdiff{B_z}{t}=-kE_0\sin(kx-\omega t)\qquad\therefore\ \pdiff{B_z}{t}=kE_0\sin(kx-\omega t) $$

We integrate with respect to $t$. Since $\displaystyle\int\sin(kx-\omega t)\,\dd t=\frac1\omega\cos(kx-\omega t)+C$ (setting $u=kx-\omega t$ gives $\dd u=-\omega\,\dd t$), and assuming there is no extra constant magnetic field apart from the wave, so that $C=0$, we obtain

$$ B_z=\frac k\omega E_0\cos(kx-\omega t) $$

Substituting into the wave equation $\pdiff{^2E_y}{x^2}=\dfrac1{c^2}\pdiff{^2E_y}{t^2}$ gives $-k^2E_0\cos(\cdots)=-\dfrac{\omega^2}{c^2}E_0\cos(\cdots)$, so $\omega=ck$, that is, $k/\omega=1/c$. Therefore we obtain

$$ \bm B=\left[0,\ 0,\ \frac{E_0}{c}\cos(kx-\omega t)\right] $$

This is the magnetic field.

  • $\bm E$ points in the $y$ direction, $\bm B$ in the $z$ direction, and the propagation direction $\bm k$ in the $x$ direction, so they are mutually perpendicular ($\bm E\perp\bm B\perp\bm k$).
  • $E_y=E_0\cos(kx-\omega t)$ and $B_z=\dfrac{E_0}{c}\cos(kx-\omega t)$ are in phase, and the ratio of their magnitudes is $E/B=c$, that is, $E=cB$.
  • $\bm E\times\bm B=[0,E_y,0]\times[0,0,B_z]=[E_yB_z,\ 0,\ 0]=\left[\dfrac{E_0^2}{c}\cos^2(kx-\omega t),\ 0,\ 0\right]$ ($\bm e_y\times\bm e_z=\bm e_x$), which always points in the $+x$ direction, that is, it coincides with the direction in which the wave travels.

Exercise 42.5: The two-dimensional wave equation with single-mode initial conditions

Solution

For the problem on the square region ($L=1$, $u=0$ on the four sides) with initial velocity $0$, the general solution, as derived in Section 42.7, is

$$ u(x,y,t)=\sum_{n=1}^\infty\sum_{m=1}^\infty D_{mn}\sin m\pi x\sin n\pi y\cos\bigl(c\pi\sqrt{m^2+n^2}\,t\bigr) $$

Substituting $t=0$ gives $\displaystyle\sum_{m,n}D_{mn}\sin m\pi x\sin n\pi y=\sin\pi x\sin2\pi y$. The functions $\sin m\pi x\sin n\pi y$ ($m,n=1,2,\ldots$) on the left-hand side are mutually orthogonal (University Mathematics, Chapter 27), and the right-hand side is itself the term with $m=1,n=2$. Determining the coefficients from orthogonality,

$$ D_{mn}=4\int_0^1\!\!\int_0^1\sin\pi x\,\sin2\pi y\,\sin m\pi x\,\sin n\pi y\,\dd x\,\dd y =4\left(\int_0^1\sin\pi x\sin m\pi x\,\dd x\right)\left(\int_0^1\sin2\pi y\sin n\pi y\,\dd y\right) $$

where $\displaystyle\int_0^1\sin p\pi x\sin q\pi x\,\dd x=\tfrac12\delta_{pq}$ ($p,q$ are positive integers), so $D_{mn}=4\cdot\tfrac12\delta_{m1}\cdot\tfrac12\delta_{n2}=\delta_{m1}\delta_{n2}$. That is, $D_{12}=1$, and all the other $D_{mn}$ are $0$. Therefore,

$$ u(x,y,t)=\sin\pi x\,\sin2\pi y\,\cos\bigl(\sqrt5\,\pi c\,t\bigr) $$

($\omega_{12}=c\pi\sqrt{1^2+2^2}=\sqrt5\,\pi c$. Here we work in units where $L=1$. For general $L$, $\omega_{12}=\sqrt5\,\pi c/L$, and the solution is $\sin\dfrac{\pi x}{L}\sin\dfrac{2\pi y}{L}\cos\left(\dfrac{\sqrt5\,\pi c}{L}t\right)$.)

Check. Wave equation: $\pdiff{^2u}{t^2}=-5\pi^2c^2u$ and $\pdiff{^2u}{x^2}+\pdiff{^2u}{y^2}=(-\pi^2-4\pi^2)u=-5\pi^2u$, so $\pdiff{^2u}{t^2}=c^2\left(\pdiff{^2u}{x^2}+\pdiff{^2u}{y^2}\right)$ holds. Boundary conditions: at $x=0,1$, $\sin\pi x=0$; at $y=0,1$, $\sin2\pi y=0$. Initial conditions: $u(x,y,0)=\sin\pi x\sin2\pi y$ and $\pdiff ut(x,y,0)=-\sqrt5\pi c\sin\pi x\sin2\pi y\sin0=0$. All are satisfied. This solution does not change its spatial shape; the whole pattern merely stretches and shrinks with angular frequency $\sqrt5\,\pi c$ (period $2/(\sqrt5\,c)$ when $L=1$), so it is a standing wave (a single normal mode).

Exercise 42.6: Energy density of a plane electromagnetic wave

Solution

The energy density is $u=\dfrac12\varepsilon_0E^2+\dfrac1{2\mu_0}B^2$. For a plane electromagnetic wave, $E=cB$, so substituting $B=E/c$ into the second term gives

$$ \frac{1}{2\mu_0}B^2=\frac{1}{2\mu_0}\cdot\frac{E^2}{c^2}=\frac{1}{2\mu_0c^2}E^2 $$

Since $c^2=\dfrac1{\varepsilon_0\mu_0}$, we have $\dfrac1{\mu_0c^2}=\varepsilon_0$, so the second term is equal to $\dfrac12\varepsilon_0E^2$, which has the same magnitude as the first term (the energy density of the electric field, $u_E$); that is, $u_B=u_E$. Therefore

$$ u=\frac12\varepsilon_0E^2+\frac12\varepsilon_0E^2=\varepsilon_0E^2 $$

This is twice the expression for the electric field alone ($u_E=\tfrac12\varepsilon_0E^2$), and it is confirmed that the electric field and the magnetic field each carry half of the energy.

Supplement. In this case the magnitude of the Poynting vector is $\abs{\bm S}=\dfrac{EB}{\mu_0}=\dfrac{E^2}{\mu_0c}=\varepsilon_0cE^2=u\,c$. That is, the relation “energy density $\times$ speed $c$ = energy flux density” holds, which expresses that energy is carried along with the electromagnetic wave at speed $c$. For time averages, $\langle u\rangle=\tfrac12\varepsilon_0E_0^2$ and $\langle S\rangle=\tfrac12\varepsilon_0cE_0^2=c\langle u\rangle$.

Exercise 42.7: Intensity of the electromagnetic wave from a point source and the amplitudes of the electric and magnetic fields

Solution

(a) The radiated power $P$ passes isotropically through a sphere of radius $r$ (area $4\pi r^2$) centered on the source. The time average $\langle S\rangle$ of the magnitude of the Poynting vector is the energy passing through unit area per unit time, so

$$ \langle S\rangle=\frac{P}{4\pi r^2}=\frac{100\ \mathrm W}{4\pi\times(2.0\ \mathrm m)^2}=\frac{100}{50.27}\ \mathrm{W/m^2}\approx1.99\ \mathrm{W/m^2} $$

(b) Solving the plane-wave intensity formula $\langle S\rangle=\tfrac12\varepsilon_0cE_0^2$ for $E_0$, we obtain

$$ E_0=\sqrt{\frac{2\langle S\rangle}{\varepsilon_0c}}=\sqrt{\frac{2\times1.989}{(8.854\times10^{-12})(2.998\times10^8)}}=\sqrt{\frac{3.979}{2.654\times10^{-3}}}\ \mathrm{V/m}=\sqrt{1.499\times10^{3}}\ \mathrm{V/m}\approx38.7\ \mathrm{V/m} $$

(c) $B_0=E_0/c=\dfrac{38.7}{2.998\times10^8}\ \mathrm T\approx1.29\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm T$ (about $0.13\ \mu\mathrm T$).

Since the intensity is proportional to $1/r^2$, the amplitudes $E_0,B_0$ decrease in inverse proportion to the distance $r$. Note that the position $2\ \mathrm m$ from the source is taken to be sufficiently far compared with the size of the source, so this is an approximation in which the spherical wave is regarded locally as a plane wave.

Exercise 42.8: Speed and wavelength of an electromagnetic wave in glass

Solution

(a) In matter, $\varepsilon_0,\mu_0$ are replaced by $\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{\mathrm r}\varepsilon_0$ and $\mu=\mu_{\mathrm r}\mu_0$, so the speed of the wave is $v=1/\sqrt{\varepsilon\mu}=c/\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm r}\mu_{\mathrm r}}=c/n$. The refractive index is

$$ n=\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm r}\mu_{\mathrm r}}=\sqrt{2.25\times1}=1.5 $$

and the speed is

$$ v=\frac cn=\frac{2.998\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s}}{1.5}\approx2.00\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

(b) In vacuum, $c=f\lambda_0$, so

$$ f=\frac{c}{\lambda_0}=\frac{2.998\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s}}{600\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm m}\approx5.0\times10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz} $$

(c) The frequency does not change in the glass either (the tangential component of the electric field must be continuous at the boundary, and the field varies in time in the same way on both sides of the boundary, so the frequency is the same before and after the boundary). From the wave relation $v=f\lambda$,

$$ \lambda=\frac vf=\frac{c/n}{c/\lambda_0}=\frac{\lambda_0}{n}=\frac{600\ \mathrm{nm}}{1.5}=400\ \mathrm{nm} $$

That is, in glass the wavelength is $1/n$ times that in vacuum (since the speed becomes $1/n$ of its vacuum value while the frequency does not change, the wavelength becomes shorter).

(Note: The relative permittivity of real glass depends on the frequency of the light (dispersion). Here we used the value $\varepsilon_{\mathrm r}=2.25$, given as the value at the frequency of this light.)

← Back to Chapter 42, “Maxwell's Equations and Electromagnetic Waves”

Chapter 43 Lagrange's Equations of Motion — Generalized Coordinates and Generalized Forces

Exercise 43.1: Generalized momenta in spherical coordinates

Solution

(1) The definition of the generalized momentum is $p_i=\partial T/\partial\dot q_i$. Differentiate $T=\tfrac12m\left(\dot r^2+r^2\dot\theta^2+r^2\sin^2\theta\,\dot\varphi^2\right)$ partially with respect to each of $\dot r,\dot\theta,\dot\varphi$. In partial differentiation, every variable other than the one being differentiated (including $r,\theta,\varphi$ themselves and the other velocities) is regarded as a constant. The only term in $T$ that contains $\dot r$ is $\tfrac12m\dot r^2$, so

$$ p_r=\pdiff{T}{\dot r}=m\dot r $$

Similarly, the only term containing $\dot\theta$ is $\tfrac12mr^2\dot\theta^2$, and the only term containing $\dot\varphi$ is $\tfrac12mr^2\sin^2\theta\,\dot\varphi^2$, so

$$ p_\theta=\pdiff{T}{\dot\theta}=mr^2\dot\theta,\qquad p_\varphi=\pdiff{T}{\dot\varphi}=mr^2\sin^2\theta\,\dot\varphi $$

As for the units, noting that angles (radians) are dimensionless, $p_r=m\dot r$ has units $\mathrm{kg\cdot m/s}$ (ordinary momentum), $p_\theta=mr^2\dot\theta$ has units $\mathrm{kg\cdot m^2/s}$ (the same dimension as angular momentum), and $p_\varphi=m(r\sin\theta)^2\dot\varphi$ has units $\mathrm{kg\cdot m^2/s}$. $p_\varphi$ is none other than the angular momentum $L_z$ about the $z$ axis for motion around a circle of radius $r\sin\theta$ (the distance from the $z$ axis) at angular velocity $\dot\varphi$.

(2) Substitute $m=2\ \mathrm{kg},\ r=3\ \mathrm m,\ \theta=\pi/3,\ \dot r=1\ \mathrm{m/s},\ \dot\theta=0.5\ \mathrm{s^{-1}},\ \dot\varphi=2\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$. We have $\sin^2(\pi/3)=(\sqrt3/2)^2=3/4$.

$$ p_r=2\times1=2\ \mathrm{kg\,m/s} $$ $$ p_\theta=2\times3^2\times0.5=2\times9\times0.5=9\ \mathrm{kg\,m^2/s} $$ $$ p_\varphi=2\times3^2\times\frac34\times2=2\times9\times0.75\times2=27\ \mathrm{kg\,m^2/s} $$

Exercise 43.2: Generalized forces for a central force

Solution

When all the forces are conservative, the generalized force is $Q_i=-\partial U/\partial q_i$. Since $U=U(r)$ does not contain $\theta,\varphi$, partial differentiation with respect to $\theta,\varphi$ gives 0:

$$ Q_r=-\pdiff{U}{r}=-\diff{U}{r},\qquad Q_\theta=-\pdiff{U}{\theta}=0,\qquad Q_\varphi=-\pdiff{U}{\varphi}=0 $$

As a check, let us also compute directly from the definition $Q_i=\sum_jF_j\,\partial x_j/\partial q_i$. The central force can be written $\bm F=-\dfrac{\dd U}{\dd r}\,\bm e_r$ (where $\bm e_r=(\sin\theta\cos\varphi,\ \sin\theta\sin\varphi,\ \cos\theta)$ is the unit vector in the radial direction). Differentiating $\bm x=r\bm e_r$ partially with respect to $\theta$ gives $\partial\bm x/\partial\theta=r\,\partial\bm e_r/\partial\theta$, which is perpendicular to $\bm e_r$ (since $\bm e_r$ is a unit vector of constant magnitude, its derivative is orthogonal to $\bm e_r$). Hence $Q_\theta=\bm F\cdot\partial\bm x/\partial\theta\propto\bm e_r\cdot\partial\bm e_r/\partial\theta=0$. The same holds for $\varphi$, so $Q_\varphi=0$. On the other hand, since $\partial\bm x/\partial r=\bm e_r$, we get $Q_r=\bm F\cdot\bm e_r=-\dd U/\dd r$, in agreement with the result above.

Physical meaning: $Q_r$ is the component of the force in the radial direction, and $Q_\theta,Q_\varphi$ are the torques (moments of force) in the respective angular directions (the same as $Q_\theta=rF_\theta$ for plane polar coordinates in Example 43.4). A central force points toward the origin, so it has no moment about the origin—that is the meaning of $Q_\theta=Q_\varphi=0$. For example, since $T$ does not contain $\varphi$, we have $\dot p_\varphi=Q_\varphi+\partial T/\partial\varphi=0$, and $p_\varphi=mr^2\sin^2\theta\,\dot\varphi$ (the angular momentum about the $z$ axis) is conserved. This is a restatement, in generalized coordinates, of what we learned in Chapters 2 and 8, that “angular momentum is conserved under a central force.”

Exercise 43.3: Terminal velocity in the frictionless case

Solution

(1) Substituting $\mu=0$ into the expression for the velocity, the terms containing $\mu mg/k$ vanish, and

$$ \dot x(t)=v_0e^{-\frac km t} $$

Since $k\gt0,\ m\gt0$, the quantity $-kt/m$ is negative, and $e^{-kt/m}\to0$ as $t\to\infty$. Therefore

$$ \lim_{t\to\infty}\dot x(t)=0 $$

(the terminal velocity is 0). Here is a physical reason why this is plausible: when there is no friction, the only horizontal force acting on the particle (point mass) is the air resistance $-k\dot x$, which always opposes the motion, so the velocity keeps decreasing. On the other hand, the smaller the velocity becomes, the smaller the resistance is, so the decrease gradually slows down, and the velocity approaches 0 exponentially (with time constant $m/k$). Since there is no other force to keep the particle moving, the final velocity can never be a value other than 0. Note also that the stopping time in the presence of friction, $t_{\text{stop}}=\dfrac mk\ln\!\left(1+\dfrac{kv_0}{\mu mg}\right)$, diverges to $\infty$ as $\mu\to0$. Without friction, the particle never comes to a complete stop in a finite time.

(2) Substituting $\mu=0$ into the expression for the position, the two terms containing $\mu$ vanish, and

$$ x(t)=\frac mkv_0\left(1-e^{-\frac km t}\right) $$

Since $e^{-kt/m}\to0$ as $t\to\infty$, the distance traveled before the particle stops is

$$ \lim_{t\to\infty}x(t)=\frac{mv_0}{k} $$

For $m=1\ \mathrm{kg},\ v_0=5\ \mathrm{m/s},\ k=0.5\ \mathrm{kg/s}$, this is $\dfrac{1\times5}{0.5}=10\ \mathrm m$. The units also work out: $\mathrm{kg\cdot(m/s)/(kg/s)}=\mathrm m$, which agrees with the dimension of length.

Exercise 43.4: The bowl problem with a nonzero initial phase

Solution

The fact that the equation of motion in the $\theta$ direction gave $\ddot\theta=0$ is due to the form $Q_\theta'=-mgR\cos\theta$, in which the external force $F_\theta=-mg\cos\theta$ cancels the tangential component of gravity, and it does not depend on the initial conditions. Therefore $\dot\theta$ is constant and remains $\dot\theta=\omega=\sqrt{g/R}$. Integrating this once gives $\theta(t)=\omega t+C$ ($C$ is the constant of integration). From the initial condition $\theta(0)=\theta_0$ we get $C=\theta_0$, so

$$ \theta(t)=\theta_0+\sqrt{\frac gR}\,t $$

The position is obtained by substituting into $x=R\cos\theta,\ y=R(1-\sin\theta)$:

$$ x=R\cos\!\left(\theta_0+\sqrt{\frac gR}\,t\right),\qquad y=R\left[1-\sin\!\left(\theta_0+\sqrt{\frac gR}\,t\right)\right] $$

(These expressions can be used while $\theta$ is in the range $0\le\theta\le\pi$, that is, while the particle moves from one rim, through the bottom, to the other rim.)

For the normal force, substituting $\dot\theta^2=g/R$ into the $r$-direction equation $N=mR\dot\theta^2+mg\sin\theta$ gives $N=mg(1+\sin\theta)$. This expression is a function of $\theta$ alone and does not contain the initial phase $\theta_0$. Therefore the expression for $N$ as a function of $\theta$ does not change. (However, the value of $\theta$ at a given time $t$ depends on $\theta_0$, so if $N$ is written as a function of time, $N(t)=mg\left[1+\sin(\theta_0+\omega t)\right]$, which depends on $\theta_0$.)

If the angular velocity is taken to be a general (constant) $\omega$, then $\ddot\theta=0$ is unchanged, and the $r$-direction equation becomes

$$ N=mR\omega^2+mg\sin\theta $$

If $\omega=\sqrt{g/R}$, then $mR\omega^2=mg$ and $N=mg(1+\sin\theta)$; if $\omega=0$ (the case in which an external force holds the particle at the same position on the surface), then

$$ N=mg\sin\theta $$

which agrees with the normal force that a stationary particle receives from the surface (the balance with the component $mg\sin\theta$ of gravity perpendicular to the surface).

Exercise 43.5: Comparing the units of generalized forces

Solution

(1) When $q=x$, the coordinates of the particle are $x_1=x,\ x_2=y$, and $\partial x/\partial x=1,\ \partial y/\partial x=0$, so from the definition $Q_i=\sum_jF_j\,\partial x_j/\partial q_i$,

$$ Q_x=F_x\cdot1+F_y\cdot0=F_x $$

The unit is $\mathrm N$, the same as that of a force.

(2) When $q=\theta$ (the rotation angle about the origin), keeping the distance $r$ from the origin constant, we have $x=r\cos\theta,\ y=r\sin\theta$. Since $\partial x/\partial\theta=-r\sin\theta=-y,\ \partial y/\partial\theta=r\cos\theta=x$,

$$ Q_\theta=F_x\pdiff{x}{\theta}+F_y\pdiff{y}{\theta}=-yF_x+xF_y $$

This is the moment of the force about the origin (the torque). The unit is $\mathrm{m\cdot N}=\mathrm{N\,m}$ (since an angle is dimensionless, the unit of $\partial x/\partial\theta$ is the unit of length, $\mathrm m$).

Reason for the difference: The generalized force $Q_i$ is defined so that the infinitesimal work is $\dd W=\sum_iQ_i\,\dd q_i$. The unit of work is always the unit of energy, $\mathrm J=\mathrm{N\,m}$, so the unit of $Q_i$ is “$\mathrm J$ divided by (the unit of $q_i$).” If $q_i=x$ (a length, unit $\mathrm m$), the unit of $Q_x$ is $\mathrm J/\mathrm m=\mathrm N$; if $q_i=\theta$ (an angle, unit $\mathrm{rad}$, dimensionless), the unit of $Q_\theta$ is $\mathrm J/\mathrm{rad}=\mathrm{N\,m}$. Depending on whether the generalized coordinate is a length or an angle, the unit of $\partial x_j/\partial q_i$ differs, and so the unit of the generalized force differs as well. Similarly, the generalized momentum is the quantity for which $p_i\dot q_i$ has the dimension of energy, and if $q_i$ is an angle, $p_i$ is an angular momentum ($\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s}$).

Exercise 43.6: A simple pendulum with air resistance

Solution

Take the pivot as the origin and the vertically downward direction as the positive direction of the $y$ axis; then the position of the bob is $(x,y)=\ell(\sin\theta,\ \cos\theta)$ (Figure 43.1). The only conservative force is gravity, and the kinetic energy is $T=\tfrac12m\ell^2\dot\theta^2$ and the potential energy is $U=-mg\ell\cos\theta$ (Example 43.6), so

$$ L=T-U=\frac12m\ell^2\dot\theta^2+mg\ell\cos\theta $$

From $\partial L/\partial\dot\theta=m\ell^2\dot\theta$ we get $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}\!\left(\pdiff{L}{\dot\theta}\right)=m\ell^2\ddot\theta$, and $\partial L/\partial\theta=-mg\ell\sin\theta$.

The nonconservative force is the air resistance, which is proportional to the speed $\ell\dot\theta$ of the bob and acts in the direction opposing the motion. The unit vector in the tangential direction in which $\theta$ increases is $\bm e_t=\pdiff{(x,y)}{\theta}\Big/\ell=(\cos\theta,\ -\sin\theta)$, so the resistive force is

$$ \bm F'=-c\ell\dot\theta\,\bm e_t $$

(If $\dot\theta\gt0$, the bob is moving in the direction in which $\theta$ increases, so the force acts in the direction of $-\bm e_t$. The expression has the same form and is also correct for $\dot\theta\lt0$.) As in the definition (Section 43.3), the generalized force is obtained by multiplying the force by $\partial(x,y)/\partial\theta=\ell\bm e_t$:

$$ Q_\theta'=\bm F'\cdot\pdiff{(x,y)}{\theta}=-c\ell\dot\theta\,\bm e_t\cdot\ell\bm e_t=-c\ell^2\dot\theta $$

(here $\bm e_t\cdot\bm e_t=1$). This is the magnitude of the moment about the pivot, namely the magnitude $c\ell\dot\theta$ of the resistive force multiplied by the arm length $\ell$. By Law 43.2,

$$ m\ell^2\ddot\theta-\left(-mg\ell\sin\theta\right)=-c\ell^2\dot\theta\quad\Longrightarrow\quad m\ell^2\ddot\theta+c\ell^2\dot\theta+mg\ell\sin\theta=0 $$

Dividing both sides by $m\ell^2$ gives

$$ \ddot\theta+\frac cm\dot\theta+\frac g\ell\sin\theta=0 $$

Unit check: since $F=c\,v$, the unit of $c$ is $\mathrm{N/(m/s)}=\mathrm{kg/s}$, so the unit of $c/m$ is $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$ and the unit of $g/\ell$ is $\mathrm{s^{-2}}$; every term is thus consistent in $\mathrm{s^{-2}}$ (angular acceleration). The tension of the string never appeared in the calculation in the first place.

When $\theta$ is small and $\sin\theta\approx\theta$ can be assumed, the equation becomes $\ddot\theta+(c/m)\dot\theta+(g/\ell)\theta=0$, which has the same form as $\ddot x+(n/m)\dot x+(k/m)x=0$, obtained by dividing the damped-oscillation equation $m\ddot x+n\dot x+kx=0$ of Section 7.3 by $m$. The discriminant of the characteristic equation is $(c/m)^2-4g/\ell$, and for $c\lt2m\sqrt{g/\ell}$ the motion is a damped oscillation.

Exercise 43.7: Checking Eq. ② in cylindrical coordinates

Solution

Compute the time derivative of $x=\rho\cos\varphi$ with the product rule and the chain rule ($\dd(\cos\varphi)/\dd t=-\dot\varphi\sin\varphi$). Since $\rho,\varphi$ are both functions of $t$,

$$ \dot x=\dot\rho\cos\varphi+\rho\,(-\dot\varphi\sin\varphi)=\dot\rho\cos\varphi-\rho\dot\varphi\sin\varphi $$

Now differentiate this partially with respect to $\dot\varphi$. Since $\rho,\varphi,\dot\rho$ are variables independent of $\dot\varphi$, they may be treated as constants. The term $\dot\rho\cos\varphi$ does not contain $\dot\varphi$, so it gives 0, and $-\rho\dot\varphi\sin\varphi$ is linear in $\dot\varphi$, so

$$ \pdiff{\dot x}{\dot\varphi}=-\rho\sin\varphi $$

On the other hand, differentiating $x=\rho\cos\varphi$ partially with respect to $\varphi$ gives

$$ \pdiff{x}{\varphi}=-\rho\sin\varphi $$

and the two are equal. That is, $\partial\dot x/\partial\dot\varphi=\partial x/\partial\varphi$ (Eq. ② of the main text) has been verified.

The other combinations are similar. We have $\partial\dot x/\partial\dot\rho=\cos\varphi=\partial x/\partial\rho$. Also, for $y=\rho\sin\varphi$, we have $\dot y=\dot\rho\sin\varphi+\rho\dot\varphi\cos\varphi$, and $\partial\dot y/\partial\dot\varphi=\rho\cos\varphi=\partial y/\partial\varphi$ and $\partial\dot y/\partial\dot\rho=\sin\varphi=\partial y/\partial\rho$. For the $z$ coordinate, $z$ itself is a generalized coordinate, so $\partial\dot z/\partial\dot z=1=\partial z/\partial z$.

Why does it hold? It holds because the equation $\dot x=\dfrac{\partial x}{\partial\rho}\dot\rho+\dfrac{\partial x}{\partial\varphi}\dot\varphi+\dfrac{\partial x}{\partial z}\dot z$ (derived in Section 43.2, the expression of $\dot\alpha_j$ in terms of the velocities of the generalized coordinates) is linear in $\dot\rho,\dot\varphi,\dot z$ and its coefficients are exactly $\partial x/\partial\rho,\ \partial x/\partial\varphi,\ \partial x/\partial z$.

Exercise 43.8: The bowl with no external force: small oscillations at the bottom, and the time from the rim to the bottom (Advanced)

Solution

(1) Let $\phi=\pi/2-\theta$; then $\sin\theta=\cos\phi$ and $\dot\theta=-\dot\phi$. The kinetic energy is $T=\tfrac12mR^2\dot\theta^2=\tfrac12mR^2\dot\phi^2$ and the potential energy is $U=mgR(1-\sin\theta)=mgR(1-\cos\phi)$, so in the absence of external forces ($Q_\phi'=0$),

$$ L=\frac12mR^2\dot\phi^2-mgR\,(1-\cos\phi) $$

From $\partial L/\partial\dot\phi=mR^2\dot\phi$ we get $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}\!\left(\pdiff{L}{\dot\phi}\right)=mR^2\ddot\phi$, and $\partial L/\partial\phi=-mgR\sin\phi$. Lagrange's equation of motion $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}\!\left(\pdiff{L}{\dot\phi}\right)-\pdiff{L}{\phi}=0$ becomes

$$ mR^2\ddot\phi+mgR\sin\phi=0\qquad\therefore\ \ddot\phi=-\frac gR\sin\phi $$

This has the same form as the equation of motion of a simple pendulum of length $R$ (Example 43.6(4)); the cross section of the bowl is a circle of radius $R$, so the particle moves along a circular arc of radius $R$. It also agrees with $\ddot\theta=(g/R)\cos\theta$ of Example 43.12, since $\ddot\theta=-\ddot\phi$ and $\sin\phi=\cos\theta$.

(2) When $\phi$ is small, $\sin\phi\approx\phi$, so $\ddot\phi=-(g/R)\phi$, which is simple harmonic motion with angular frequency $\omega=\sqrt{g/R}$. The period is

$$ T_0=\frac{2\pi}{\omega}=2\pi\sqrt{\frac Rg} $$

which is the same as the period of small oscillations of a simple pendulum of length $R$. For $R=0.50\ \mathrm m$, $T_0=2\pi\sqrt{0.50/9.8}\approx1.42\ \mathrm s$, and $1/4$ of this is $0.355\ \mathrm s$.

(3) The rim is at $\phi=\pi/2$, and since the particle is released gently, $\dot\phi=0$. By conservation of mechanical energy (the normal force does no work), for any $\phi$ we have

$$ \frac12mR^2\dot\phi^2+mgR(1-\cos\phi)=mgR(1-\cos\tfrac\pi2)=mgR $$

Rearranging gives $\tfrac12mR^2\dot\phi^2=mgR\cos\phi$, that is, $\dot\phi^2=\dfrac{2g}{R}\cos\phi$. Since $\phi$ decreases from the rim toward the bottom, $\dot\phi=-\sqrt{2g\cos\phi/R}$, and separating variables and integrating from $\phi=\pi/2$ to $0$, the time required is

$$ t=\int_{\pi/2}^{0}\frac{\dd\phi}{-\sqrt{2g\cos\phi/R}}=\sqrt{\frac{R}{2g}}\int_0^{\pi/2}\frac{\dd\phi}{\sqrt{\cos\phi}} $$

The integrand of the integral on the right-hand side diverges at $\phi=\pi/2$, but the integral itself is finite, and numerical integration gives $\approx2.622$ (it can be written as $\sqrt2\,K(1/\sqrt2)$ using the complete elliptic integral of the first kind). Therefore, for $R=0.50\ \mathrm m,\ g=9.8\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$,

$$ t=\sqrt{\frac{0.50}{2\times9.8}}\times2.622=0.1597\ldots\ \mathrm s\times2.622\approx0.419\ \mathrm s $$

This is about 18% longer than the time $0.355\ \mathrm s$ for the case in which the angular velocity is kept constant by an external force (Example 43.11(1)). The time from the rim to the bottom without external force does not coincide with $1/4$ of the period of small oscillations in (2) ($0.355\ \mathrm s$). This is because, when the amplitude is large ($\phi_0=\pi/2$), the period of a simple pendulum becomes longer than the period of small oscillations. On the other hand, the time of the motion kept at $\dot\theta=\sqrt{g/R}$ by the external force is $0.355\ \mathrm s$ merely as a result of choosing the angular velocity to be the angular frequency of small oscillations.

← Back to Chapter 43, “Lagrange's Equations of Motion — Generalized Coordinates and Generalized Forces”

Chapter 44 Applications of the Lagrangian Formalism and Variational Principles

Exercise 44.1: Simple harmonic motion with two springs (in series)

Solution

Let $x$ be the displacement of the particle (point mass) and $s$ the elongation of the first spring (the one on the wall side). Then the elongation of the second spring is $x-s$. The kinetic energy is only the $\frac12m\dot x^2$ of the particle (the connection point has zero mass), and the potential energy is the sum of the elastic energies of the two springs, so the Lagrangian is

$$ L=\frac12m\dot x^2-\frac12k_1s^2-\frac12k_2(x-s)^2 $$

First we write down Lagrange's equation of motion for the coordinate $s$. Since $L$ does not contain $\dot s$, $\partial L/\partial\dot s=0$; moreover $\partial L/\partial s=-k_1s+k_2(x-s)$ (differentiating $-\frac12k_2(x-s)^2$ with respect to $s$ gives $+k_2(x-s)$), so

$$ \diff{}{t}\left(\pdiff{L}{\dot s}\right)-\pdiff{L}{s}=0-\big[-k_1s+k_2(x-s)\big]=0 \qquad\therefore\ k_1s=k_2(x-s) $$

This expresses the balance of forces at the massless connection point (the force with which the first spring pulls equals the force with which the second spring pulls). Solving it for $s$ gives

$$ s=\frac{k_2}{k_1+k_2}\,x,\qquad x-s=\frac{k_1}{k_1+k_2}\,x $$

Next, the equation for the coordinate $x$: since $\partial L/\partial\dot x=m\dot x$ and $\partial L/\partial x=-k_2(x-s)$,

$$ \diff{}{t}(m\dot x)+k_2(x-s)=0 \qquad\therefore\ m\ddot x+k_2\cdot\frac{k_1}{k_1+k_2}\,x=0 $$

Hence

$$ m\ddot x+\frac{k_1k_2}{k_1+k_2}\,x=0 $$

which has the form of the equation of motion of simple harmonic motion, $m\ddot x=-k_{\text{eq}}x$. Therefore

$$ k_{\text{eq}}=\frac{k_1k_2}{k_1+k_2}\ \left(\text{that is,}\ \frac1{k_{\text{eq}}}=\frac1{k_1}+\frac1{k_2}\right),\qquad \omega=\sqrt{\frac{k_{\text{eq}}}{m}}=\sqrt{\frac{k_1k_2}{m(k_1+k_2)}} $$

These are the results (the high school physics formula for the “equivalent spring constant of springs in series” has come out of Lagrange's equations of motion). Note also that eliminating $s$ by substituting the above relation into $L$ gives $L=\frac12m\dot x^2-\frac12\dfrac{k_1k_2}{k_1+k_2}x^2$, which leads to the same result.

Numerical values: $k_{\text{eq}}=\dfrac{30\times60}{30+60}\ \mathrm{N/m}=20\ \mathrm{N/m}$, $\omega=\sqrt{\dfrac{20\ \mathrm{N/m}}{0.20\ \mathrm{kg}}}=\sqrt{100}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}=10\ \mathrm{rad/s}$, and the period is $T_0=2\pi/\omega=0.628\ \mathrm s$.

Exercise 44.2: Simple pendulum: comparing rectangular and polar coordinates, and finding the tension

Solution

(1) In rectangular coordinates, $T=\frac12m(\dot x^2+\dot y^2)$, and the potential energy is $U=-mgy$ (since $y$ is positive downward). If we write the Lagrangian without taking the constraint into account, we have

$$ L=\frac12m(\dot x^2+\dot y^2)+mgy $$

If we apply Lagrange's equations of motion to $x,y$ as they stand, we obtain $m\ddot x=0,\ m\ddot y=mg$, that is, unconstrained free fall. This is because the condition $x^2+y^2=\ell^2$, that the length of the rod is constant, has not been included; to incorporate it, we use the method of Lagrange multipliers of Section 44.7 (Example 44.11).

(2) In polar coordinates, $T=\frac12m(\dot r^2+r^2\dot\theta^2)$ and $U=-mgr\cos\theta$ (the particle is lower than the fixed point by $r\cos\theta$), so

$$ L=\frac12m(\dot r^2+r^2\dot\theta^2)+mgr\cos\theta $$

The partial derivatives are $\partial L/\partial\dot r=m\dot r$, $\partial L/\partial r=mr\dot\theta^2+mg\cos\theta$, $\partial L/\partial\dot\theta=mr^2\dot\theta$, and $\partial L/\partial\theta=-mgr\sin\theta$. The tension $N$ acts in the radial direction and pulls the particle toward the origin (the direction in which $r$ decreases), so $Q_r'=-N$; it has no component in the $\theta$ direction, so $Q_\theta'=0$. From Lagrange's equations of motion with nonconservative forces,

\begin{align*} r\text{-direction:}&\quad m\ddot r-mr\dot\theta^2-mg\cos\theta=-N\\ \theta\text{-direction:}&\quad \diff{}{t}(mr^2\dot\theta)+mgr\sin\theta=0 \end{align*}

Substituting the constraint $r=\ell$ ($\dot r=\ddot r=0$), the $r$-direction equation becomes $-m\ell\dot\theta^2-mg\cos\theta=-N$, that is,

$$ N=m\ell\dot\theta^2+mg\cos\theta $$

and the $\theta$-direction equation becomes $m\ell^2\ddot\theta+mg\ell\sin\theta=0$, that is, $\ddot\theta=-(g/\ell)\sin\theta$. Both agree with the results of Section 44.2.3 and Example 44.11 ($\ddot\theta=-(g/\ell)\sin\theta$, $N=mg\cos\theta+m\ell\dot\theta^2$).

(3) For $x=\ell\sin\theta,\ y=\ell\cos\theta$, we have $\dot x=\ell\dot\theta\cos\theta,\ \dot y=-\ell\dot\theta\sin\theta$, so $\dot x^2+\dot y^2=\ell^2\dot\theta^2(\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta)=\ell^2\dot\theta^2$. Also, $mgy=mg\ell\cos\theta=mg\ell-mg\ell(1-\cos\theta)$, so

$$ L=\frac12m\ell^2\dot\theta^2+mg\ell\cos\theta =\left[\frac12m\ell^2\dot\theta^2-mg\ell(1-\cos\theta)\right]+mg\ell $$

which differs from the $L$ of Section 44.2.3 only by the constant $mg\ell$. A constant vanishes upon differentiation with respect to $\theta$ or $\dot\theta$, so it does not affect the equation of motion, and the same $\ddot\theta=-(g/\ell)\sin\theta$ is obtained.

Even when we change the choice of coordinates (only $\theta$, with the constraint built in from the start; $(r,\theta)$, keeping the constraint and adding the generalized force $Q_r'=-N$ of the tension; or $(x,y)$, treating the constraint with a multiplier), the procedure of “forming $L$ and substituting it into an equation of the same form” does not change, and we arrive at the same equation of motion—this is advantage (III) of Section 44.1.

Exercise 44.3: Energy conservation in planetary motion

Solution

Differentiate $E=\frac12m\big(\dot r^2+r^2\dot\theta^2\big)-\dfrac{GMm}{r}$ with respect to time. By the product rule and the rule for differentiating a composite function,

$$ \diff{E}{t}=m\dot r\ddot r+mr\dot r\dot\theta^2+mr^2\dot\theta\ddot\theta+\frac{GMm}{r^2}\dot r $$

(here we used $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}\left(\dfrac12mr^2\dot\theta^2\right)=mr\dot r\dot\theta^2+mr^2\dot\theta\ddot\theta$ and $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd t}\left(-\dfrac{GMm}{r}\right)=+\dfrac{GMm}{r^2}\dot r$). Regrouping this into the terms containing $\dot r$ and the terms containing $\dot\theta$, we obtain

$$ \diff{E}{t}=m\dot r\left(\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2+\frac{GM}{r^2}\right)+mr\dot\theta\left(2\dot r\dot\theta+r\ddot\theta\right) $$

(expanding the right-hand side gives $m\dot r\ddot r-mr\dot r\dot\theta^2+\frac{GMm}{r^2}\dot r+2mr\dot r\dot\theta^2+mr^2\dot\theta\ddot\theta$, and since $-mr\dot r\dot\theta^2+2mr\dot r\dot\theta^2=mr\dot r\dot\theta^2$, this agrees with the expression above). By the equation of motion $m(\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2)=-GMm/r^2$, the first parenthesis is $0$, and by the equation of motion $m(2\dot r\dot\theta+r\ddot\theta)=0$, the second parenthesis is also $0$. Therefore $\dd E/\dd t=0$, that is, $E$ is constant.

For the circular orbit $r=R$ ($\dot r=0$), using $\dot\theta=\Omega$ and $\Omega^2=GM/R^3$ (Example 44.5), the kinetic energy is $T=\frac12mR^2\Omega^2=\frac12mR^2\cdot\dfrac{GM}{R^3}=\dfrac{GMm}{2R}$ and the potential energy is $U=-\dfrac{GMm}{R}$, so

$$ E=T+U=\frac{GMm}{2R}-\frac{GMm}{R}=-\frac{GMm}{2R} $$

$E$ is negative, which expresses that the planet is bound to the Sun. Also, the relation $E=-T=U/2$ holds.

Exercise 44.4: Equations of motion and conservation of angular momentum for a general central-force potential

Solution

Take the partial derivatives of $L=\frac12m\big(\dot r^2+r^2\dot\theta^2\big)-U(r)$ treating $r,\dot r,\theta,\dot\theta$ as independent variables. The only term containing $\dot r$ is $\frac12m\dot r^2$; the terms containing $r$ are $\frac12mr^2\dot\theta^2$ and $-U(r)$; the only term containing $\dot\theta$ is $\frac12mr^2\dot\theta^2$; and there is no term in $L$ containing $\theta$ itself ($U$ is a function of $r$ only). Hence

$$ \pdiff{L}{\dot r}=m\dot r,\qquad \pdiff{L}{r}=mr\dot\theta^2-\diff{U}{r},\qquad \pdiff{L}{\dot\theta}=mr^2\dot\theta,\qquad \pdiff{L}{\theta}=0 $$

Lagrange's equations of motion are

\begin{align*} r\text{-direction:}&\quad \diff{}{t}(m\dot r)-\left(mr\dot\theta^2-\diff{U}{r}\right)=0 \ \Longrightarrow\ m\big(\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2\big)=-\diff{U}{r}\\ \theta\text{-direction:}&\quad \diff{}{t}\big(mr^2\dot\theta\big)-0=0 \end{align*}

The right-hand side $-\dd U/\dd r$ of the $r$-direction equation is exactly the radial component $F_r$ of the central force (if $U=-GMm/r$, then $-\dd U/\dd r=-GMm/r^2$, which agrees with the equation of Section 44.3). The $\theta$-direction equation, regardless of the specific form of $U(r)$, means

$$ \diff{}{t}\big(mr^2\dot\theta\big)=0\qquad\therefore\ p_\theta=mr^2\dot\theta=\text{constant} $$

The reason angular momentum is conserved for any central force is that $L$ does not depend on $\theta$ itself (that is, $L$ does not change when the coordinate system is rotated).

Exercise 44.5: Why is the fixed-endpoint condition $\delta q(t_1)=\delta q(t_2)=0$ needed?

Solution

If we carry out the same calculation as in Section 44.5 without imposing the fixed-endpoint condition, the boundary term produced by integration by parts remains, and

$$ \delta S=\left[\pdiff{L}{\dot q}\,\delta q\right]_{t_1}^{t_2}+\int_{t_1}^{t_2}\left(\pdiff{L}{q}-\diff{}{t}\left(\pdiff{L}{\dot q}\right)\right)\delta q\,\dd t $$

The first term is the difference, between the two endpoints, of the product of the generalized momentum $p=\partial L/\partial\dot q$ and the displacement $\delta q$ at the endpoint.

Take the free particle ($L=\frac12m\dot x^2$) as an example. The actual motion is uniform rectilinear motion $x=v_0t$ ($v_0\ne0$), and since $\ddot x=0$, the integrand of the second term is 0. As the displacement, choose for example $\delta x(t)=ct$ ($c$ is a small constant); this is 0 at $t=0$, but at $t=t_2$ it is $ct_2\ne0$ (the final point is not fixed). Then

$$ \delta S=\int_0^{t_2}m\dot x\,\diff{(\delta x)}{t}\,\dd t=\int_0^{t_2}mv_0c\,\dd t=mv_0c\,t_2=\Big[m\dot x\,\delta x\Big]_0^{t_2} $$

so only the boundary term $p\,\delta x$ remains, and it does not become $0$. That is, if we do not fix the position of the final point and demand “$\delta S=0$ for any displacement $\delta x(t_2)$,” we must have $p(t_2)=mv_0=0$ (the momentum is 0 at the final point). This contradicts the motion of a particle that is actually moving ($v_0\ne0$). Therefore, Hamilton's principle that “the actual motion is stationary” must be stated within the family of paths with fixed endpoints.

Conversely, if we restrict ourselves to the family with fixed endpoints, the boundary term vanishes, the conclusion that “the coefficient of the integrand is identically 0” (the fundamental lemma of the calculus of variations) can be used, and the Euler–Lagrange equation is obtained. Physically, fixing the endpoints corresponds to the given data of “where the particle was at times $t_1,t_2$.” Incidentally, the boundary term $p\,\delta q$ expresses how much the action changes when the position of the final point is slightly changed, and this quantity plays an important role in the Hamilton–Jacobi equation (Section 46.5).

Exercise 44.6: Why can't the frictional force be derived from a potential energy?

Solution

A conservative force is a force for which the work done by the force in moving between two points A and B is determined only by the positions of the two endpoints, independent of the path. Only in that case is the potential energy $U(\bm r)$, defined as the work from a reference point, uniquely determined, and we can write $\bm F=-\nabla U$ (Section 4.4).

The kinetic friction force has magnitude $\mu mg$, and its direction is always opposite to the direction of motion. Therefore, the work done by kinetic friction, with $s$ the total length of the path, is $W=-\mu mg\,s$, which is proportional to the length of the path. Putting in numbers, $\mu mg=0.30\times1.0\ \mathrm{kg}\times9.8\ \mathrm{m/s^2}=2.94\ \mathrm N$, and

\begin{align*} \text{(a) Straight line (}s=2.0\ \mathrm m\text{):}&\quad W=-2.94\ \mathrm N\times2.0\ \mathrm m=-5.88\ \mathrm J\\ \text{(b) Semicircle (radius }1.0\ \mathrm m\text{, so }s=\pi\times1.0\ \mathrm m=3.14\ \mathrm m\text{):}&\quad W=-2.94\ \mathrm N\times3.14\ \mathrm m=-9.24\ \mathrm J \end{align*}

Even for the same movement from A to B the work differs, so the “work from A to B” is not determined by the positions of the endpoints alone. Hence a potential energy as a function of position alone cannot be defined, and friction is a nonconservative force. Moreover, the direction of the frictional force is determined not by position but by the direction of motion (even at the same position, motion in the opposite direction gives a force in the opposite direction), so it is not a function $\bm F(\bm r)$ of position alone in the first place. For this reason, Lagrange's equations of motion cannot be used as they stand in the form with $L=T-U$, and friction must be added on the right-hand side as a generalized force $Q_i'$ (Section 43.5).

Exercise 44.7: An example where “stationary,” not “minimum,” is the right name (Advanced)

Solution

(1) Under the endpoint conditions $x(0)=x(\tau)=0$, the only solution of the equation of motion $\ddot x=-\omega^2x$ when $\omega\tau\ne n\pi$ is $x=0$ (in the general solution $x=C_1\cos\omega t+C_2\sin\omega t$, $x(0)=0$ gives $C_1=0$, and $x(\tau)=0$ gives $C_2\sin\omega\tau=0$; since $\sin\omega\tau\ne0$, we get $C_2=0$). The action of $x=0$ is $S=0$.

The displaced path $x_\varepsilon=\varepsilon\sin(\pi t/\tau)$ satisfies $x_\varepsilon(0)=x_\varepsilon(\tau)=0$. Since $\dot x_\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\pi/\tau)\cos(\pi t/\tau)$, the action is

$$ S(\varepsilon)=\int_0^\tau\left[\frac12m\varepsilon^2\frac{\pi^2}{\tau^2}\cos^2\frac{\pi t}{\tau}-\frac12m\omega^2\varepsilon^2\sin^2\frac{\pi t}{\tau}\right]\dd t $$

Using $\int_0^\tau\cos^2(\pi t/\tau)\,\dd t=\int_0^\tau\sin^2(\pi t/\tau)\,\dd t=\tau/2$ (by the half-angle formula $\cos^2\phi=(1+\cos2\phi)/2$ and so on), we obtain

$$ S(\varepsilon)=\frac12m\varepsilon^2\frac{\pi^2}{\tau^2}\cdot\frac\tau2-\frac12m\omega^2\varepsilon^2\cdot\frac\tau2 =\frac{m\varepsilon^2\tau}{4}\left[\left(\frac\pi\tau\right)^2-\omega^2\right] $$

Note also that there is no term of first order in $\varepsilon$ ($x=0$ is a stationary point).

(2) For $\varepsilon\ne0$ we have $m\varepsilon^2\tau/4>0$, so the sign of $S(\varepsilon)$ is determined by the sign of $(\pi/\tau)^2-\omega^2$.

• When $\omega\tau<\pi$ ($\tau$ is shorter than the half period $\pi/\omega$), $S(\varepsilon)>0=S(0)$, so $x=0$ minimizes the action (for displacements of this form). For example, for $m=1\ \mathrm{kg}$, $\tau=1\ \mathrm s$, $\omega=2\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, $\varepsilon=0.1\ \mathrm m$, we have $S=\dfrac{0.01\times1}{4}(\pi^2-4)=0.0147\ \mathrm{J\,s}>0$.

• When $\omega\tau>\pi$ ($\tau$ is longer than the half period), $S(\varepsilon)<0=S(0)$, so there exists a path with a smaller action than $x=0$. Therefore $x=0$ is not a minimum. For example, for $\omega=4\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, $S=\dfrac{0.01}{4}(\pi^2-16)=-0.0153\ \mathrm{J\,s}<0$. The same calculation shows that the action of the $n$th-order displacement $\varepsilon\sin(n\pi t/\tau)$ is $\dfrac{m\varepsilon^2\tau}{4}\big[(n\pi/\tau)^2-\omega^2\big]$, so if $\pi<\omega\tau<2\pi$, the action decreases for the $n=1$ displacement and increases for displacements with $n\ge2$. That is, $x=0$ is a saddle point, neither a local minimum nor a local maximum.

• When $\omega\tau=\pi$, the coefficient is 0 and the second-order change is also 0 (a time interval of a half period marks the boundary at which the action ceases to be a minimum).

From the above, we see that the essence of Hamilton's principle is “stationary” ($\delta S=0$), not “minimum,” and that there are examples in which, when the time interval is long, the action ceases to be a minimum.

Exercise 44.8: The shape of a soap film (catenoid) — an application of the Beltrami identity

Solution

Since $F=y\sqrt{1+y'^2}$ (the constant $2\pi$ does not affect the stationarity condition, so it has been dropped) does not contain $x$ explicitly, the Beltrami identity $F-y'\partial F/\partial y'=$ constant can be used. Since $\partial F/\partial y'=y\,y'/\sqrt{1+y'^2}$,

$$ F-y'\pdiff{F}{y'}=y\sqrt{1+y'^2}-\frac{y\,y'^2}{\sqrt{1+y'^2}} =\frac{y\big[(1+y'^2)-y'^2\big]}{\sqrt{1+y'^2}}=\frac{y}{\sqrt{1+y'^2}}=C $$

(where $C>0$ is a constant of integration). Squaring both sides and rearranging gives $y^2=C^2(1+y'^2)$, that is, $y'^2=y^2/C^2-1$, so

$$ y'=\sqrt{\frac{y^2}{C^2}-1}\qquad(y\ge C) $$

(we consider the side with $y'\ge0$). Separating variables gives $\dfrac{\dd y}{\sqrt{y^2/C^2-1}}=\dd x$. If we set $u=y/C$, the left-hand side becomes $C\dfrac{\dd u}{\sqrt{u^2-1}}$; using $\displaystyle\int\frac{\dd u}{\sqrt{u^2-1}}=\operatorname{arcosh}u$ (University Mathematics, Section 2.6), we obtain

$$ C\operatorname{arcosh}\frac yC=x-x_0\qquad\therefore\ y=C\cosh\frac{x-x_0}{C} $$

(here $x_0$ is a constant of integration).

Check: when $y=C\cosh\frac{x-x_0}{C}$, we have $y'=\sinh\frac{x-x_0}{C}$, so $\sqrt{1+y'^2}=\cosh\frac{x-x_0}{C}$ and $y/\sqrt{1+y'^2}=C$, which indeed satisfies the identity above. Also, $y'^2=\sinh^2=\cosh^2-1=y^2/C^2-1$. The surface obtained by rotating this curve about the axis is the shape of the soap film (the catenoid). The constants $C,x_0$ are determined by two conditions, the radii and the positions of the two end rings. For two rings of the same radius $R$, if the separation exceeds about $1.33R$, no solution of this form exists, and the film breaks.

Exercise 44.9: Planetary motion: correspondence between the abstract form and the substituted form

Solution

(1) Take the partial derivatives of $L=\frac12m(\dot r^2+r^2\dot\theta^2)+GMm/r$ treating $r,\dot r,\theta,\dot\theta$ as independent variables.

Quantity in abstract formExplicit form
$\partial L/\partial\dot r$, and then $\diff{}{t}(\partial L/\partial\dot r)$$m\dot r$, and then $m\ddot r$
$\partial L/\partial r$$mr\dot\theta^2-GMm/r^2$
$\partial L/\partial\dot\theta$, and then $\diff{}{t}(\partial L/\partial\dot\theta)$$mr^2\dot\theta$, and then $m(2r\dot r\dot\theta+r^2\ddot\theta)$ (product rule)
$\partial L/\partial\theta$$0$ ($\theta$ itself does not appear in $L$)

(Differentiating $GMm/r=GMm\,r^{-1}$ with respect to $r$ gives $-GMm\,r^{-2}$.) Substituting these, the $r$-direction equation is $m\ddot r-mr\dot\theta^2+GMm/r^2=0$ and the $\theta$-direction equation is $m(2r\dot r\dot\theta+r^2\ddot\theta)=0$, which agree with the results of Section 44.3.

(2) Rewrite the $r$-direction equation as $m\ddot r=mr\dot\theta^2-GMm/r^2$. The left-hand side $m\ddot r=\dot p_r$ is the time rate of change of the radial momentum. The first term on the right, $mr\dot\theta^2=\partial T/\partial r$, is a term corresponding to the centrifugal force that appears because we describe the motion in polar coordinates; it points outward (positive). The second term, $-GMm/r^2=Q_r=-\partial U/\partial r$, is the force of universal gravitation, which acts toward the Sun (the direction in which $r$ decreases, negative). This is a concrete example of the fact that in general we can write $\dot p_i=Q_i+\partial T/\partial q_i$.

(3) For a circular orbit, $\dot r=\ddot r=0$, so from the $r$-direction equation $-mr\Omega^2+GMm/r^2=0$, that is, $\Omega^2=GM/r^3$. Putting in numbers, $GM=6.674\times10^{-11}\times1.989\times10^{30}=1.3275\times10^{20}\ \mathrm{m^3/s^2}$ and $r^3=(1.496\times10^{11})^3=3.348\times10^{33}\ \mathrm{m^3}$, so

$$ \Omega=\sqrt{\frac{1.3275\times10^{20}}{3.348\times10^{33}}}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}=1.99\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm{rad/s},\qquad P=\frac{2\pi}{\Omega}=3.16\times10^7\ \mathrm s $$

Indeed, $3.16\times10^7\ \mathrm s\div(86400\ \mathrm{s/day})=365$ days, which is one year.

Exercise 44.10: Does the principle of least action mean that “nature has a purpose”?

Solution

(1) The method using Snell's law applies, in turn, a local rule at each boundary (“determine the next direction from the angle of incidence at the present point”) and builds up the path of the light little by little from the starting point. Fermat's principle is a global formulation: fix the starting point and the end point in advance, and choose the path for which the travel time along the whole path is stationary. As we saw in Section 44.6.3, $n\sin\theta=$ constant is derived from Fermat's principle by the calculus of variations, so the two formulations are mathematically different expressions of the same content.

(2) Once the initial position and velocity are given, the actual motion is uniquely determined, all the way into the future, by the equation of motion at each instant alone. No such function as “knowing the final point” is needed there. Hamilton's principle sounds as if it were “choosing the final point and minimizing the action” because, as the conditions that specify the motion, it chooses to give “the positions of the starting point and the final point” instead of “the initial position and velocity.” It is merely specifying the same motion again by different conditions. Furthermore, the stationary value is not necessarily a minimum (Exercise 44.7), and when the time interval is long it is not even a minimum, so the teleological interpretation that “nature aims at the minimum” does not hold as it stands. In modern physics, $\delta S=0$ is understood as an alternative expression of the same content as the local equation of motion, written as a condition on the whole path.

← Back to Chapter 44, “Applications of the Lagrangian Formalism and Variational Principles”

Chapter 45 Hamilton's Canonical Equations and Phase Space

Exercise 45.1: Legendre transformation of a Lagrangian with a linear term

Solution

Differentiating $L(\dot q)=\tfrac12 m\dot q^2-c\dot q$ with respect to $\dot q$, the generalized momentum is

$$ p=\pdiff{L}{\dot q}=m\dot q-c $$

Solving this for $\dot q$ gives $\dot q=(p+c)/m$. Substituting this into the Legendre transformation $H=p\dot q-L$, we obtain

$$ H=p\cdot\frac{p+c}{m}-\left[\frac12 m\left(\frac{p+c}{m}\right)^2-c\cdot\frac{p+c}{m}\right] $$

Simplifying inside the brackets, using $\tfrac12 m(p+c)^2/m^2=(p+c)^2/(2m)$, we have

$$ H=\frac{p(p+c)}{m}-\frac{(p+c)^2}{2m}+\frac{c(p+c)}{m} =\frac{(p+c)(p+c)}{m}-\frac{(p+c)^2}{2m} =\frac{(p+c)^2}{m}-\frac{(p+c)^2}{2m} $$

(In the middle step we combined the terms as $p(p+c)+c(p+c)=(p+c)(p+c)=(p+c)^2$). Therefore,

$$ H(p)=\frac{(p+c)^2}{2m} $$

Setting $c=0$, we can confirm that we recover the same form as Example 45.1 ($H=p^2/(2m)$, with $a\to m$). The linear term $-c\dot q$ has the effect of shifting the origin of momentum by $p\to p+c$.

Exercise 45.2: The general derivation of the canonical equations (review)

Solution

This exercise is practice in reproducing the derivation in Section 45.2 by yourself. We restate only the key points.

Differentiate the defining equation directly. Differentiating $H=\sum_i p_i\dot q_i-L(q,\dot q,t)$, the product rule gives

$$ \dd H=\sum_i\left(\dot q_i\,\dd p_i+p_i\,\dd\dot q_i\right)-\dd L $$

On the other hand, the total differential of $L$ is $\dd L=\sum_i(\partial L/\partial q_i)\dd q_i+\sum_i(\partial L/\partial\dot q_i)\dd\dot q_i+(\partial L/\partial t)\dd t=\sum_i(\partial L/\partial q_i)\dd q_i+\sum_i p_i\,\dd\dot q_i+(\partial L/\partial t)\dd t$ (we used the definition of the generalized momentum, $p_i=\partial L/\partial\dot q_i$), so substituting this gives

$$ \dd H=\sum_i\dot q_i\,\dd p_i-\sum_i\pdiff{L}{q_i}\dd q_i-\pdiff{L}{t}\dd t $$

and the terms $\sum_i p_i\,\dd\dot q_i$ cancel exactly (this is the essential effect of the Legendre transformation).

Use the fact that $H$ is a function of $(q,p,t)$. On the other hand, from the formula for the total differential of a function of several variables, the total differential of $H(q,p,t)$ can also be written as

$$ \dd H=\sum_i\pdiff{H}{q_i}\dd q_i+\sum_i\pdiff{H}{p_i}\dd p_i+\pdiff{H}{t}\dd t $$

Comparing the coefficients of the independent infinitesimal quantities $\dd q_i,\dd p_i,\dd t$ in the two expressions, we obtain the identities of the Legendre transformation

$$ \pdiff{H}{p_i}=\dot q_i,\qquad \pdiff{H}{q_i}=-\pdiff{L}{q_i},\qquad \pdiff{H}{t}=-\pdiff{L}{t} $$

Finally, from Lagrange's equation $\dd(\partial L/\partial\dot q_i)/\dd t=\partial L/\partial q_i$ and the definition of the generalized momentum, we have $\partial L/\partial q_i=\dot p_i$, so the second equation becomes $\dot p_i=-\partial H/\partial q_i$. In this way, Hamilton's canonical equations

$$ \dot q_i=\pdiff{H}{p_i},\qquad \dot p_i=-\pdiff{H}{q_i} $$

are derived. Note that Lagrange's equation (the equation of motion) is used only once, at the very end; the identities before that hold independently of the equation of motion.

As an example with explicit time dependence, take the forced oscillation $L=\tfrac12 m\dot q^2-\tfrac12 kq^2+qF_0\cos\omega t$. Then $p=m\dot q$ and $H=p^2/(2m)+\tfrac12kq^2-qF_0\cos\omega t$, and $\partial H/\partial t=qF_0\omega\sin\omega t=-\partial L/\partial t$ indeed holds.

Exercise 45.3: The number and order of the equations

Solution

The general solution of a single second-order linear ordinary differential equation (for example, $\ddot x+\omega^2x=0$) contains exactly two arbitrary constants ($x=C_1\cos\omega t+C_2\sin\omega t$). This reflects the fact that “finding a function that satisfies a second-order differential equation corresponds to integrating twice, and each integration produces one arbitrary constant.” In the Lagrangian formalism with $n$ generalized coordinates, there is one second-order equation for each coordinate, $n$ equations in all, so the total number of arbitrary constants is $2n$—in terms of initial conditions, these are the initial position $q_i(0)$ and the initial velocity $\dot q_i(0)$ of each coordinate, $2n$ in total.

On the other hand, the general solution of a single first-order linear ordinary differential equation (for example, $\dot y+ay=0$) contains only one arbitrary constant (because a single integration suffices). In the Hamiltonian formalism, there is one first-order equation for each of the $2n$ variables $q_i$ and $p_i$ taken together, $2n$ equations in all, so the total number of arbitrary constants is again $2n$—in terms of initial conditions, these are the initial values $q_i(0),p_i(0)$ of the variables, $2n$ in total.

Therefore, “$n$ second-order equations (a total of $2n$ constants)” and “$2n$ first-order equations (a total of $2n$ constants)” are exactly the same in the amount of information needed to determine the motion uniquely, and the physics that can be described does not change whichever form we use. The only difference is in how that information is carried: whether it is “distributed two to each of the $n$ coordinates” or “one to each of the $2n$ variables” (that is, configuration space or phase space).

Exercise 45.4: The Hamiltonian formalism for a two-dimensional isotropic harmonic oscillator

Solution

The kinetic energy is $T=\tfrac12 m(\dot x^2+\dot y^2)$ and the potential energy is $U=\tfrac12 k(x^2+y^2)$, and the constraints do not depend explicitly on time, so $H=T+U$ holds (Theorem 45.2). Since the generalized momenta are $p_x=m\dot x,\ p_y=m\dot y$, we can rewrite $T=(p_x^2+p_y^2)/(2m)$, and

$$ H=\frac{p_x^2}{2m}+\frac{p_y^2}{2m}+\frac12 k(x^2+y^2) $$

The canonical equations, written for each of $x,y$ separately, are

$$ \dot x=\pdiff{H}{p_x}=\frac{p_x}{m},\qquad \dot p_x=-\pdiff{H}{x}=-kx, \qquad \dot y=\pdiff{H}{p_y}=\frac{p_y}{m},\qquad \dot p_y=-\pdiff{H}{y}=-ky $$

four equations in all. Combining the two equations for $x$ gives $\ddot x=-(k/m)x$, and combining the two equations for $y$ gives $\ddot y=-(k/m)y$; $y$ does not appear at all in the equation for $x$, and $x$ does not appear at all in the equation for $y$. That is, the Hamiltonian $H$ of the two-dimensional isotropic harmonic oscillator can be decomposed into the sum $H=H_x+H_y$ of the one-dimensional harmonic oscillator in the $x$ direction, $H_x=p_x^2/(2m)+\tfrac12 kx^2$, and the one-dimensional harmonic oscillator in the $y$ direction, $H_y=p_y^2/(2m)+\tfrac12 ky^2$, and the two directions oscillate independently of each other (with exactly the same mathematics as in Example 45.2).

Exercise 45.5: Conservation of angular momentum for a general central force

Solution

When the potential energy is a function $U(r)$ of the radial coordinate only (containing no angle $\theta$—this is precisely the condition for a “central force”), the kinetic energy is exactly the same as in the case of universal gravitation, $T=\tfrac12 m(\dot r^2+r^2\dot\theta^2)$, so the Hamiltonian is

$$ H=\frac{p_r^2}{2m}+\frac{p_\theta^2}{2mr^2}+U(r) $$

(here $p_r=m\dot r,\ p_\theta=mr^2\dot\theta$, the same as in Example 45.7). Looking at this $H$, we see that, regardless of the specific form of $U(r)$, $\theta$ does not appear explicitly anywhere:

$$ \pdiff{H}{\theta}=0 $$

Therefore, from the canonical equations, $\dot p_\theta=-\partial H/\partial\theta=0$, that is, $p_\theta=mr^2\dot\theta$ is constant. This is a proof, in the Hamiltonian formalism, that conservation of angular momentum follows solely from the geometric property that “a central force is always directed toward the origin”—whether $U(r)$ is the universal-gravitation form $-GMm/r$, the Hooke's-law form $\tfrac12 kr^2$, or any other function of $r$ alone, the argument is unchanged. For reference, let us also find the radial equation of motion for a general $U(r)$: combining $\dot p_r=-\partial H/\partial r=p_\theta^2/(mr^3)-U'(r)$ with $\dot r=p_r/m$, we obtain

$$ \ddot r=r\dot\theta^2-\frac{U'(r)}{m} $$

(When $U(r)=-GMm/r$, we have $U'(r)=GMm/r^2$, which agrees with the result of Example 45.7, $\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2=-GM/r^2$.)

Exercise 45.6: The energy and momentum of the separatrix of a simple pendulum

Solution

(1) Substituting $\ell=0.5\ \mathrm{m}$, $m=0.2\ \mathrm{kg}$, and $g=9.8\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$, the energy of the separatrix is

$$ E_{\mathrm{sep}}=2mg\ell=2\times0.2\times9.8\times0.5=1.96\ \mathrm{J}\approx2.0\ \mathrm{J} $$

(2) When the maximum swing angle is $\theta_0=150^\circ$, the energy is, from $\cos150^\circ=-\cos30^\circ=-\sqrt3/2\approx-0.8660$,

$$ E=mg\ell(1-\cos\theta_0)=0.2\times9.8\times0.5\times(1-(-0.8660)) =0.98\times1.8660\approx1.8\ \mathrm{J} $$

Since $E\approx1.8\ \mathrm{J}$ is smaller than $E_{\mathrm{sep}}\approx2.0\ \mathrm{J}$ ($E\lt E_{\mathrm{sep}}$), by the classification in Section 45.5.2 this motion is a libration. The bob swings back and forth over the range $\pm150^\circ$ centered on the lowest point, without reaching the top ($\theta=\pm180^\circ$) (since the bob is on the end of a rod, it can be lifted to $150^\circ$ and released gently). Note that as $\theta_0$ is brought closer to $180^\circ$, $E$ approaches $E_{\mathrm{sep}}$, and at $\theta_0=180^\circ$ (the limit in which the bob comes to rest exactly at the top) we have exactly $E=E_{\mathrm{sep}}$.

(3) For $\theta_0=60^\circ$ ($\cos60^\circ=0.5$),

$$ E=mg\ell(1-\cos60^\circ)=0.2\times9.8\times0.5\times0.5=0.49\ \mathrm{J} $$

At the lowest point $\theta=0$ we have $U=0$, so $H=p_\theta^2/(2m\ell^2)=E$. Since $m\ell^2=0.2\times0.5^2=0.050\ \mathrm{kg\,m^2}$,

$$ p_\theta=\sqrt{2m\ell^2E}=\sqrt{2\times0.050\times0.49}=\sqrt{0.049}\approx0.22\ \mathrm{kg\,m^2/s} $$

(At this point the angular velocity is $\dot\theta=p_\theta/(m\ell^2)\approx4.4\ \mathrm{rad/s}$ and the speed of the bob is $\ell\dot\theta\approx2.2\ \mathrm{m/s}$, which agrees with $\tfrac12m(\ell\dot\theta)^2\approx0.49\ \mathrm J$.)

← Back to Chapter 45, “Hamilton's Canonical Equations and Phase Space”

Chapter 46 Symmetry and Conservation Laws, Poisson Brackets, and Canonical Transformations

Exercise 46.1: Cyclic coordinates and conserved momenta

Solution

Differentiating the Lagrangian $L=\frac12m(\dot x^2+\dot y^2)-U(x)$ partially with respect to $y$, we note that $U(x)$ does not contain $y$, and the kinetic-energy term contains only $\dot y$ (not $y$ itself), so

$$ \pdiff{L}{y}=0 $$

This is precisely the condition for a cyclic coordinate in Definition 46.1 (this chapter), so $y$ is a cyclic coordinate. By Theorem 46.1, the conjugate momentum

$$ p_y=\pdiff{L}{\dot y}=m\dot y $$

is constant in time, that is, conserved (this can also be verified directly: substituting $\pdiff{L}{y}=0$ into the Lagrange equation for the $y$ direction, $\dd(m\dot y)/\dd t-\pdiff{L}{y}=0$, gives $\dd(m\dot y)/\dd t=0$). This is a natural result, corresponding to the fact that $U$ depends only on $x$ and no force acts in the $y$ direction (the system is uniform in the $y$ direction).

Exercise 46.2: The energy function coincides with $H=T+U$

Solution

$T=\frac12\sum_{i,j}m_{ij}(q)\dot q_i\dot q_j$ ($m_{ij}=m_{ji}$) is a homogeneous function of degree 2 in the $\dot q_i$. In general, for a homogeneous function $f(\dot q)$ of degree $n$ (a function such that $f\to\lambda^nf$ when all the $\dot q_i$ are multiplied by $\lambda$), Euler's theorem on homogeneous functions, $\sum_i\dot q_i\pdiff{f}{\dot q_i}=nf$, holds. Indeed, differentiating $T$ partially with respect to $\dot q_k$ (noting that $m_{ij}=m_{ji}$) gives

$$ \pdiff{T}{\dot q_k}=\sum_im_{ik}\dot q_i $$

so that

$$ \sum_k\dot q_k\pdiff{T}{\dot q_k}=\sum_{k,i}m_{ik}\dot q_i\dot q_k=2T $$

(the last equality is just the definition $T=\frac12\sum_{i,k}m_{ik}\dot q_i\dot q_k$). This is Euler's theorem for the case $n=2$. Since $U=U(q)$ does not depend on $\dot q$, we have $\pdiff{U}{\dot q_i}=0$; hence, for $L=T-U$,

$$ h=\sum_i\dot q_i\pdiff{L}{\dot q_i}-L=\sum_i\dot q_i\pdiff{T}{\dot q_i}-(T-U)=2T-(T-U)=T+U $$

and $h=T+U$ has been shown (a calculation with a concrete symmetric matrix $m_{ij}$ for two degrees of freedom gives the same result).

Exercise 46.3: Does a double pendulum have a cyclic coordinate?

Solution

The standard Lagrangian of a double pendulum of length $\ell$ and mass $m$ (deflection angles $\theta_1,\theta_2$) is

$$ L=m\ell^2\dot\theta_1^2+\frac12m\ell^2\dot\theta_2^2+m\ell^2\dot\theta_1\dot\theta_2\cos(\theta_1-\theta_2)+2mg\ell\cos\theta_1+mg\ell\cos\theta_2 $$

Differentiating this partially with respect to each of $\theta_1,\theta_2$ gives

$$ \pdiff{L}{\theta_1}=-2mg\ell\sin\theta_1-m\ell^2\dot\theta_1\dot\theta_2\sin(\theta_1-\theta_2),\qquad \pdiff{L}{\theta_2}=-mg\ell\sin\theta_2+m\ell^2\dot\theta_1\dot\theta_2\sin(\theta_1-\theta_2) $$

and neither is zero in general. For example, substituting $\theta_1=\theta_2=\pi/2,\ \dot\theta_1=\dot\theta_2=0$ gives $\pdiff{L}{\theta_1}=-2mg\ell\neq0$ ($\pdiff{L}{\theta_1}$ vanishes only for special values such as $\theta_1=\theta_2=0$ with zero angular velocities; it is not identically zero). Therefore neither of $\theta_1,\theta_2$ is a cyclic coordinate. In planetary motion (Section 46.1 of this chapter), $\theta$ became a cyclic coordinate because universal gravitation does not depend on the azimuthal angle $\theta$ (there is rotational symmetry); in the double pendulum, however, gravity points in a particular direction, vertically downward, so the deflection angles $\theta_1,\theta_2$ themselves appear in the potential energy, and there is no rotational symmetry. This example teaches the lesson that, in general, there are not necessarily as many cyclic coordinates as degrees of freedom; the number is determined by what symmetries the system has. Incidentally, for a three-dimensional spherical pendulum (polar angle $\theta$, azimuthal angle $\varphi$), gravity is symmetric under rotations about the vertical axis, so the Lagrangian $\frac12m\ell^2(\dot\theta^2+\sin^2\theta\,\dot\varphi^2)+mg\ell\cos\theta$ does not contain $\varphi$, and $\varphi$ becomes a cyclic coordinate (the corresponding conserved quantity $p_\varphi=m\ell^2\sin^2\theta\,\dot\varphi$ is the angular momentum about the vertical axis).

Exercise 46.4: A potential that depends explicitly on time, and non-conservation of energy

Solution

As a concrete example, consider a spring pendulum whose spring constant varies periodically with time, $U(x,t)=\frac12k_0\bigl(1+\cos\omega_{\rm d}t\bigr)x^2$ ($k_0,\omega_{\rm d}$ are constants; this represents, for example, a situation in which the stiffness of the spring fluctuates slowly because of temperature changes and the like). The Lagrangian is $L=\frac12m\dot x^2-U(x,t)$, and differentiating it partially with respect to the time $t$ gives

$$ \pdiff{L}{t}=-\pdiff{U}{t}=\frac12k_0\omega_{\rm d}x^2\sin\omega_{\rm d}t $$

This is in general not zero (when $x\neq0$ and $\sin\omega_{\rm d}t\neq0$). Therefore, by Theorem 46.2 of this chapter (the equation $\dd h/\dd t=-\pdiff{L}{t}$), the energy function $h=\frac12m\dot x^2+U(x,t)$ is not conserved:

$$ \diff{h}{t}=-\pdiff{L}{t}=-\frac12k_0\omega_{\rm d}x^2\sin\omega_{\rm d}t\neq0 $$

Physically, the operation of externally varying the stiffness of the spring does work on the pendulum or takes energy from it, so it is natural that energy is not conserved when we look at the pendulum alone (of course, energy is conserved for the whole system including the external operator). This confirms, with a different concrete example, the same kind of phenomenon as in Example 46.5 of this chapter (the simple pendulum whose support is shaken).

Exercise 46.5: Direct check of the antisymmetry of the Poisson bracket

Solution

We start from the definition of the Poisson bracket (the equation $\{A,B\}=\sum_i(\partial_{q_i}A\,\partial_{p_i}B-\partial_{p_i}A\,\partial_{q_i}B)$ of this chapter). Writing out $\{B,A\}$ according to the same definition, we have

$$ \{B,A\}=\sum_i\left(\pdiff{B}{q_i}\pdiff{A}{p_i}-\pdiff{B}{p_i}\pdiff{A}{q_i}\right) $$

Now, looking closely at each term on the right-hand side, we see that the products of the same two factors that appear in the definition of $\{A,B\}$ simply appear with their order changed (the value is unchanged, because the order of multiplication can be exchanged): $\pdiff{B}{q_i}\pdiff{A}{p_i}=\pdiff{A}{p_i}\pdiff{B}{q_i}$, $\pdiff{B}{p_i}\pdiff{A}{q_i}=\pdiff{A}{q_i}\pdiff{B}{p_i}$. Therefore,

$$ \{B,A\}=\sum_i\left(\pdiff{A}{p_i}\pdiff{B}{q_i}-\pdiff{A}{q_i}\pdiff{B}{p_i}\right)=-\sum_i\left(\pdiff{A}{q_i}\pdiff{B}{p_i}-\pdiff{A}{p_i}\pdiff{B}{q_i}\right)=-\{A,B\} $$

and $\{A,B\}=-\{B,A\}$ has been shown directly from the definition (letting $A=B$ gives $\{A,A\}=-\{A,A\}$, that is, $2\{A,A\}=0$, so $\{A,A\}=0$ follows at the same time).

Exercise 46.6: Derivation of the unified formula for time evolution

Solution

Suppose that $A(q,p)$ does not contain the time $t$ explicitly. Taking the total differential of $A(q(t),p(t))$ with respect to the time $t$ by the differentiation rule for composite functions (the chain rule), we obtain

$$ \diff{A}{t}=\sum_i\left(\pdiff{A}{q_i}\dot q_i+\pdiff{A}{p_i}\dot p_i\right) $$

Substituting the canonical equations $\dot q_i=\pdiff{H}{p_i},\ \dot p_i=-\pdiff{H}{q_i}$ here, we obtain

$$ \diff{A}{t}=\sum_i\left(\pdiff{A}{q_i}\pdiff{H}{p_i}-\pdiff{A}{p_i}\pdiff{H}{q_i}\right) $$

The right-hand side is nothing other than the definition of the Poisson bracket (with $B=H$):

$$ \diff{A}{t}=\{A,H\} $$

This is Theorem 46.6 of this chapter, derived on our own from the beginning for the case in which $A$ does not contain $t$ explicitly ($\partial A/\partial t=0$) (for example, in the concrete case $A=qp$ with $H$ taken to be the Hamiltonian of simple harmonic motion, $\dd A/\dd t$ calculated by the chain rule and the Poisson bracket $\{A,H\}$ both equal $p^2/m-kq^2$, and they agree).

Exercise 46.7: The condition for $\{L_z,H\}=0$

Solution

First we compute $\{L_z,H\}$ from Definition 46.3. The partial derivatives of $L_z=xp_y-yp_x$ are

$$ \pdiff{L_z}{x}=p_y,\qquad\pdiff{L_z}{y}=-p_x,\qquad\pdiff{L_z}{p_x}=-y,\qquad\pdiff{L_z}{p_y}=x,\qquad\pdiff{L_z}{z}=\pdiff{L_z}{p_z}=0 $$

Setting $(q_1,q_2,q_3)=(x,y,z)$ and $(p_1,p_2,p_3)=(p_x,p_y,p_z)$ and substituting into Definition 46.3, we obtain

$$ \{L_z,H\}=\left(\pdiff{L_z}{x}\pdiff{H}{p_x}+\pdiff{L_z}{y}\pdiff{H}{p_y}\right)-\left(\pdiff{L_z}{p_x}\pdiff{H}{x}+\pdiff{L_z}{p_y}\pdiff{H}{y}\right) =p_y\pdiff{H}{p_x}-p_x\pdiff{H}{p_y}+y\pdiff{H}{x}-x\pdiff{H}{y} $$

that is,

$$ \{L_z,H\}=y\pdiff{H}{x}-x\pdiff{H}{y}+p_y\pdiff{H}{p_x}-p_x\pdiff{H}{p_y} $$

The meaning of this expression becomes clear if we calculate the change in $H$ under an infinitesimal rotation about the $z$ axis, $x\to x-\varepsilon y,\ y\to y+\varepsilon x,\ p_x\to p_x-\varepsilon p_y,\ p_y\to p_y+\varepsilon p_x$ (positions and momenta rotate in the same way). By the chain rule,

$$ \delta H=\varepsilon\left(-y\pdiff{H}{x}+x\pdiff{H}{y}-p_y\pdiff{H}{p_x}+p_x\pdiff{H}{p_y}\right)=-\varepsilon\{L_z,H\} $$

Therefore,

$H$ is invariant under rotations about the $z$ axis ($\delta H=0$, rotational symmetry) $\iff$ $\{L_z,H\}=0$ ($L_z$ is a conserved quantity)

and this equivalence holds in general, not only for $H$ of a particular form. Let us check it with concrete examples.

(i) For a central force, $H=\dfrac{p_x^2+p_y^2+p_z^2}{2m}+U(r)$, $r=\sqrt{x^2+y^2+z^2}$, we have $\pdiff{H}{x}=U'(r)\dfrac{x}{r},\ \pdiff{H}{y}=U'(r)\dfrac{y}{r},\ \pdiff{H}{p_x}=\dfrac{p_x}{m},\ \pdiff{H}{p_y}=\dfrac{p_y}{m}$, so

$$ \{L_z,H\}=yU'\frac{x}{r}-xU'\frac{y}{r}+p_y\frac{p_x}{m}-p_x\frac{p_y}{m}=0 $$

A similar calculation gives $\{L_x,H\}=\{L_y,H\}=0$ as well, so for a central force all three components of the angular momentum are conserved.

(ii) Even when $H$ contains a mixed term such as $xp_x+yp_y$, this quantity is invariant under rotations, so $\{L_z,H\}=0$ (from $\pdiff{H}{x}=p_x,\ \pdiff{H}{y}=p_y,\ \pdiff{H}{p_x}=x,\ \pdiff{H}{p_y}=y$ we get $yp_x-xp_y+p_yx-p_xy=0$). A “function of $x^2+y^2$ and $p_x^2+p_y^2$ only” is merely one example of a rotation-invariant $H$, and a rotation-invariant $H$ is not limited to this form.

(iii) If $cx$ ($c$ is a constant; this is the potential corresponding to a uniform force in the $x$ direction) is added to $H$, then $c$ is added to $\pdiff{H}{x}$, so $\{L_z,H\}=cy\neq0$. The rotational symmetry is broken, and $L_z$ is not conserved: it changes at the rate $\dd L_z/\dd t=\{L_z,H\}=cy$ (this agrees with the moment $xF_y-yF_x=cy$ of the force $-c\,\bm e_x$).

Exercise 46.8: Show that $Q=q,\ P=p+f(q)$ is always a canonical transformation

Solution

We compute the criterion $\{Q,P\}=1$ directly from the definition. From $Q=q$ we get $\pdiff{Q}{q}=1,\ \pdiff{Q}{p}=0$. From $P=p+f(q)$ we get $\pdiff{P}{q}=f'(q)$ (the ordinary derivative of $f$ with respect to $q$) and $\pdiff{P}{p}=1$. Substituting these,

$$ \{Q,P\}=\pdiff{Q}{q}\pdiff{P}{p}-\pdiff{Q}{p}\pdiff{P}{q}=1\times1-0\times f'(q)=1 $$

so that, whatever (differentiable) function $f(q)$ is, $\{Q,P\}=1$ always holds. Therefore $Q=q,\ P=p+f(q)$ is always a canonical transformation. This corresponds to taking $Q=q$ ($Q'=1$) and setting $g(q)=f(q)$ in the general form of the point transformation, $P=p/Q'(q)+g(q)$ (Section 46.4.1). This transformation adds to the momentum a “shift determined by the coordinate alone,” and it is another typical way of constructing canonical transformations, paired with the point transformation seen in Example 46.9 of this chapter (a transformation that relabels only the coordinates). (Physically, it has the same mathematical structure as the fact that the canonical momentum $p=m\dot q+eA(q)$ of a charged particle (charge $e$) in an electromagnetic field ($A$ is the vector potential) has the form of the mechanical momentum $m\dot q$ plus a term determined by the position alone—we do not go into this within the scope of the electromagnetism in this book, but it is one example showing that the idea of canonical transformations appears in such situations.)

Exercise 46.9: The effective potential and the types of orbits

Solution

(1) From $U_{\rm eff}'(r)=-\dfrac{p_\theta^2}{mr^3}+\dfrac{GMm}{r^2}=0$, the radius of the circular orbit is

$$ r_0=\frac{p_\theta^2}{GMm^2} $$

The value there follows from $\dfrac{p_\theta^2}{2mr_0^2}=\dfrac{G^2M^2m^3}{2p_\theta^2}$ and $\dfrac{GMm}{r_0}=\dfrac{G^2M^2m^3}{p_\theta^2}$:

$$ E_{\min}=U_{\rm eff}(r_0)=\frac{G^2M^2m^3}{2p_\theta^2}-\frac{G^2M^2m^3}{p_\theta^2}=-\frac{G^2M^2m^3}{2p_\theta^2}=-\frac{GMm}{2r_0} $$

(the second derivative $U_{\rm eff}''(r_0)=GMm/r_0^3\gt0$ shows that this is a minimum point).

(2) The graph has the shape of a valley: as $r\to0$ the centrifugal term $p_\theta^2/(2mr^2)\to+\infty$; as $r\to\infty$, $U_{\rm eff}\to0$ (it approaches from the negative side because $-GMm/r$ dominates); and at $r=r_0$ it takes the minimum value $E_{\min}\lt0$ (Figure 8.5). Since the radial motion obeys $E=\frac12m\dot r^2+U_{\rm eff}(r)$, the condition $\dot r^2\ge0$ means that the particle can move only in the range $E\ge U_{\rm eff}(r)$, and the intersections of the horizontal line at $E$ with the curve are the turning points. Solving $E=U_{\rm eff}(r)$ as the quadratic equation in $u=1/r$, $\frac{p_\theta^2}{2m}u^2-GMmu-E=0$, we obtain

$$ u=\frac{m}{p_\theta^2}\left[GMm\pm\sqrt{G^2M^2m^2+\frac{2Ep_\theta^2}{m}}\right] $$

as the two roots.

  • (a) $E=E_{\min}$: the quantity under the square root is 0, and there is only one turning point, $r=r_0$. A circular orbit that stays at $r=r_0$.
  • (b) $E_{\min}\lt E\lt0$: the quantity under the square root is positive, and both roots for $u$ are positive, so there are two turning points of $r$, $r_{\min}\lt r_0\lt r_{\max}$ (the perihelion and the aphelion). An elliptical orbit (bound orbit) confined to the range $r_{\min}\le r\le r_{\max}$.
  • (c) $E=0$: the roots are $u=2GMm^2/p_\theta^2$ (a positive root) and $u=0$, so there is only one turning point, $r_{\min}=p_\theta^2/(2GMm^2)=r_0/2$, and the particle can go out to $r\to\infty$. A parabolic orbit (the boundary between bound and unbound).
  • (d) $E\gt0$: there is only one positive root $u$ (the other root is negative), so there is only one turning point, $r_{\min}\lt r_0/2$, and the particle can go out to $r\to\infty$. A hyperbolic orbit (unbound orbit).

This corresponds to the fact that, in terms of the eccentricity $\varepsilon=\sqrt{1+E/|E_{\min}|}$ (a rewriting of the relation $E=-\frac{GMm}{2r_0}(1-\varepsilon^2)$ in Section 8.4), we have $\varepsilon=0$ (circle), $0\lt\varepsilon\lt1$ (ellipse), $\varepsilon=1$ (parabola), and $\varepsilon\gt1$ (hyperbola).

Exercise 46.10: The generating function $F=qQ$ and the transformation of simple harmonic motion

Solution

(1) Substituting $F=qQ$ into the equations $p=\pdiff{F}{q},\ P=-\pdiff{F}{Q}$, we obtain

$$ p=\pdiff{(qQ)}{q}=Q,\qquad P=-\pdiff{(qQ)}{Q}=-q $$

that is, $Q=p,\ P=-q$ (since $\dfrac{\partial^2F}{\partial q\,\partial Q}=1\neq0$, the equations can be solved for $Q$).

(2) From $\pdiff{Q}{q}=0,\ \pdiff{Q}{p}=1,\ \pdiff{P}{q}=-1,\ \pdiff{P}{p}=0$,

$$ \{Q,P\}=\pdiff{Q}{q}\pdiff{P}{p}-\pdiff{Q}{p}\pdiff{P}{q}=0\times0-1\times(-1)=1 $$

holds.

(3) Since $F$ does not contain $t$, $K=H$ (in the equation $K=H+\pdiff{F}{t}$, $\pdiff{F}{t}=0$). Substituting $q=-P,\ p=Q$ into $H=\dfrac{p^2}{2m}+\dfrac12kq^2$ gives

$$ K(Q,P)=\frac{Q^2}{2m}+\frac12kP^2 $$

The new canonical equations are $\dot Q=\pdiff{K}{P}=kP$ and $\dot P=-\pdiff{K}{Q}=-\dfrac{Q}{m}$. Returning to the original variables, $\dot Q=\dot p=-kq=kP$ (which agrees with the original equation of motion $\dot p=-kq$) and $\dot P=-\dot q=-\dfrac{p}{m}=-\dfrac{Q}{m}$ (which agrees with the original $\dot q=p/m$), so there is no contradiction. Even when the roles are exchanged, the equations of motion keep the same form (the canonical equations).

Exercise 46.11: $\rho=\rho(H)$ is a stationary distribution

Solution

(1) Differentiating $\rho=\rho(H(q,p))$ partially, the chain rule gives $\pdiff{\rho}{q_i}=\rho'(H)\pdiff{H}{q_i}$ and $\pdiff{\rho}{p_i}=\rho'(H)\pdiff{H}{p_i}$. Substituting these into the definition of $\{\rho,H\}$, we obtain
