$$ \theta=\frac1{1+\dfrac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{p\lambda_{\mathrm T}^3}e^{-\varepsilon_0/k_{\mathrm B}T}}=\frac1{1+p_0/p}=\frac p{p+p_0},\qquad p_0=\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{\lambda_{\mathrm T}^3}e^{-\varepsilon_0/k_{\mathrm B}T} $$

Here we multiplied the numerator and denominator by $p$. For $p\ll p_0$, $\theta\approx p/p_0$ (proportional to the pressure), and for $p\gg p_0$, $\theta\to1$ (saturation). $p_0$ is the pressure at which $\theta=\tfrac12$.

(2) $m=39.948\times1.6605\times10^{-27}=6.634\times10^{-26}\ \mathrm{kg}$ and $k_{\mathrm B}T=1.3806\times10^{-23}\times300=4.142\times10^{-21}\ \mathrm J=0.02585\ \mathrm{eV}$. The thermal de Broglie wavelength is

$$ \lambda_{\mathrm T}=\frac h{\sqrt{2\pi mk_{\mathrm B}T}}=\frac{6.626\times10^{-34}}{\sqrt{2\pi\times6.634\times10^{-26}\times4.142\times10^{-21}}}\ \mathrm m=1.595\times10^{-11}\ \mathrm m $$

Hence $\lambda_{\mathrm T}^3=4.056\times10^{-33}\ \mathrm{m^3}$ and $k_{\mathrm B}T/\lambda_{\mathrm T}^3=4.142\times10^{-21}/4.056\times10^{-33}=1.021\times10^{12}\ \mathrm{Pa}$. The exponential factor is $e^{-0.40/0.02585}=e^{-15.47}=1.907\times10^{-7}$, so

$$ p_0=1.021\times10^{12}\ \mathrm{Pa}\times1.907\times10^{-7}=1.95\times10^{5}\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx1.9\ \mathrm{atm} $$

At $1\ \mathrm{atm}=1.013\times10^5\ \mathrm{Pa}$ the coverage is $\theta=1.013\times10^5/(1.013\times10^5+1.947\times10^5)=0.342$. The pressure at which $\theta=0.9$ follows from $p/(p+p_0)=0.9$: $p=9p_0=1.75\times10^6\ \mathrm{Pa}\approx17\ \mathrm{atm}$.

Exercise 52.2: Distribution of the number of particles in an ideal gas

Solution

(1) The probability that the number of particles is exactly $N$ is the sum of the probabilities of all states $j$ of the $N$-particle system, $P(N)=\sum_je^{-\beta(E_{j,N}-\mu N)}/\Xi=z^NZ_N/\Xi$. Using $Z_N=Z_1^N/N!$, $\Xi=e^{zZ_1}$, and $\bar N=zZ_1$, we obtain

$$ P(N)=\frac{(zZ_1)^N}{N!}e^{-zZ_1}=\frac{\bar N^N}{N!}e^{-\bar N}=\frac{4^N}{N!}e^{-4} $$

(This is the Poisson distribution.)

(2) $e^{-4}=0.018316$. $P(0)=e^{-4}=0.0183$ and $P(4)=\dfrac{4^4}{4!}e^{-4}=\dfrac{256}{24}\times0.018316=10.667\times0.018316=0.1954$. $P(N\ge8)=1-\sum_{N=0}^{7}P(N)$. The sum of $P(0),\ldots,P(7)=0.0183,\ 0.0733,\ 0.1465,\ 0.1954,\ 0.1954,\ 0.1563,\ 0.1042,\ 0.0595$ is $0.9489$, so $P(N\ge8)=1-0.9489=0.0511$. For a mean of $4$, the probability that $8$ or more particles are present is about $5\,\%$.

(3) $\langle N(N-1)\rangle=e^{-\bar N}\sum_{N\ge2}N(N-1)\dfrac{\bar N^N}{N!}=e^{-\bar N}\sum_{N\ge2}\dfrac{\bar N^N}{(N-2)!}=e^{-\bar N}\bar N^2\sum_{M\ge0}\dfrac{\bar N^M}{M!}=\bar N^2$ (we put $N-2=M$; the terms with $N=0,1$ vanish because $N(N-1)=0$). Therefore $\langle N^2\rangle=\langle N(N-1)\rangle+\langle N\rangle=\bar N^2+\bar N$, and the variance is $\langle N^2\rangle-\bar N^2=\bar N$.

(4) The number density of air is $n=p/k_{\mathrm B}T=2.446\times10^{25}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$. From $\bar N=nV=4$, $V=4/n=1.635\times10^{-25}\ \mathrm{m^3}$. The edge length of the cube is $V^{1/3}=5.47\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm m\approx5.5\ \mathrm{nm}$. In such a tiny region, the relative fluctuation of the number of particles is as large as $1/\sqrt{\bar N}=50\,\%$.

Exercise 52.3: Putting two particles into three states with energies $0,\varepsilon,2\varepsilon$

Solution

(1)–(3) The energy of the two particles is the sum of the energies of the states they occupy. (1) For distinguishable particles, each particle independently occupies one of the three states (with weights $1,x,x^2$), so $Z=(1+x+x^2)^2=1+2x+3x^2+2x^3+x^4$. (2) For bosons there are the six cases of Example 52.7, $(0,0),(0,\varepsilon),(0,2\varepsilon),(\varepsilon,\varepsilon),(\varepsilon,2\varepsilon),(2\varepsilon,2\varepsilon)$, with energies $0,\varepsilon,2\varepsilon,2\varepsilon,3\varepsilon,4\varepsilon$, so $Z_{\mathrm B}=1+x+2x^2+x^3+x^4$. (3) For fermions there are three cases, $(0,\varepsilon),(0,2\varepsilon),(\varepsilon,2\varepsilon)$, with energies $\varepsilon,2\varepsilon,3\varepsilon$, so $Z_{\mathrm F}=x+x^2+x^3$.

(4) The mean energy is $\langle E\rangle=\sum E\,e^{-\beta E}/Z$. For $k_{\mathrm B}T=\varepsilon$, $x=e^{-1}=0.36788$, $x^2=0.13534$, $x^3=0.04979$, and $x^4=0.01832$. (1): The numerator is $0\cdot1+1\cdot2x+2\cdot3x^2+3\cdot2x^3+4x^4=0.7358+0.8120+0.2987+0.0733=1.9198$ and $Z=1+0.7358+0.4060+0.0996+0.0183=2.2597$, so $\langle E\rangle=0.850\,\varepsilon$. (2): The numerator is $x+2\cdot2x^2+3x^3+4x^4=0.3679+0.5413+0.1494+0.0733=1.1318$ and $Z_{\mathrm B}=1.7067$, so the mean energy is $0.663\,\varepsilon$. (3): The numerator is $x+2x^2+3x^3=0.3679+0.2707+0.1494=0.7879$ and $Z_{\mathrm F}=0.5530$, so the mean energy is $1.425\,\varepsilon$. As $T\to0$ ($x\to0$), the state of lowest energy dominates: in (1) and (2), $\langle E\rangle\to0$ (both particles go into the ground state $0$); in (3), the exclusion principle forbids both particles from entering the ground state, so the system settles in the lowest allowed state $(0,\varepsilon)$ and $\langle E\rangle\to\varepsilon$.

(5) The states in which the two particles are in the same state are, in (1), the three states $(0,0),(\varepsilon,\varepsilon),(2\varepsilon,2\varepsilon)$, whose weights sum to $1+x^2+x^4=1.1537$, so the probability is $1.1537/2.2597=0.511$. In (2), the same three states also have total weight $1.1537$, so the probability is $1.1537/1.7067=0.676$. For bosons, the probability that the two particles occupy the same state is larger than for distinguishable particles ($0.511$). This is a numerical confirmation of the statement “indistinguishable bosons tend to gather in the same state.”

Exercise 52.4: Occupation probability of electrons in the conduction band of a semiconductor

Solution

At $T=300\ \mathrm K$, $k_{\mathrm B}T=0.02585\ \mathrm{eV}$. The difference between the bottom of the conduction band and $\mu$ is $E_{\mathrm g}/2$, so $x=(E_{\mathrm g}/2)/k_{\mathrm B}T$, and the occupation probability is $f=1/(e^x+1)$.

(1) Silicon $E_{\mathrm g}/2=0.56\ \mathrm{eV}$ and $x=0.56/0.02585=21.66$. Since $e^{21.66}=2.556\times10^{9}$, $f=1/(2.556\times10^{9}+1)=3.91\times10^{-10}$. The Boltzmann factor is $e^{-21.66}=3.91\times10^{-10}$; because $e^x\gg1$, the $1$ in the denominator contributes only a relative error of $10^{-9}$, so the two are practically identical.

(2) Germanium $E_{\mathrm g}/2=0.33\ \mathrm{eV}$, $x=12.77$, and $f=2.86\times10^{-6}$ (the Boltzmann factor is $e^{-12.77}=2.86\times10^{-6}$). The narrower the band gap, the more easily electrons are excited; the value is about $7000$ times larger than for silicon.

Probability of a hole The top of the valence band lies $E_{\mathrm g}/2$ below $\mu$. The probability that it is empty is $1-f(\mu-E_{\mathrm g}/2)$, and by the symmetry $f(\mu+\delta)+f(\mu-\delta)=1$ (Section 52.5), $1-f(\mu-\delta)=f(\mu+\delta)$. That is, for $\delta=E_{\mathrm g}/2$ it has the same value as the occupation probability of the bottom of the conduction band ($3.9\times10^{-10}$ for Si and $2.9\times10^{-6}$ for Ge). Each time one electron is excited into the conduction band, one hole is created in the valence band, so this is a natural result.

Exercise 52.5: Occupation probability of a donor level — counting the spin degeneracy

Solution

(1) Let $x=(E_{\mathrm d}-\mu)/k_{\mathrm B}T$. There are three states: empty (electron number $0$, energy $0$), and one electron with spin up or spin down (electron number $1$, energy $E_{\mathrm d}$, two ways). The grand partition function is

$$ \Xi_{\mathrm d}=1+2e^{-\beta(E_{\mathrm d}-\mu)}=1+2e^{-x} $$

The probability that one electron is present is $2e^{-x}/\Xi_{\mathrm d}$, and the electron number in that case is $1$, so

$$ \langle n_{\mathrm d}\rangle=\frac{2e^{-x}}{1+2e^{-x}}=\frac{1}{1+\tfrac12e^{x}} $$

(here we divided the numerator and denominator by $2e^{-x}$). The same result is obtained from $\langle n_{\mathrm d}\rangle=k_{\mathrm B}T\,\partial\ln\Xi_{\mathrm d}/\partial\mu$.

(2) $x=0.030/0.02585=1.160$ and $e^x=3.191$, so

$$ \langle n_{\mathrm d}\rangle=\frac1{1+\tfrac12\times3.191}=\frac1{1+1.596}=0.385 $$

The ordinary Fermi distribution, which does not take the spin degeneracy into account, gives $1/(e^x+1)=1/(3.191+1)=0.239$, so the value that counts the degeneracy is about $1.6$ times larger.

(3) Because there are two ways for an electron to occupy the donor level, spin up and spin down, the weight of the “electron present” state is doubled, and the level is more easily occupied. Since this can be written as $\tfrac12e^{x}=e^{x-\ln2}$, it has the same effect as decreasing $E_{\mathrm d}-\mu$ by $k_{\mathrm B}T\ln2=0.018\ \mathrm{eV}$. The ordinary Fermi distribution is the probability that a single state with a specified spin is occupied; for a level like a donor, which “can accept an electron in only one of the two spin states (not in both at once),” this factor of $\tfrac12$ appears.

Exercise 52.6: Two-dimensional electron gas

Solution

(1) For a square of side $L$ (area $L^2$), the allowed wavenumbers are $k_x=n_x\pi/L$ and $k_y=n_y\pi/L$ ($n_x,n_y=1,2,\ldots$), and one lattice point in wavenumber space occupies an area $(\pi/L)^2$. The states whose wavenumber magnitude is at most $k$ are the lattice points in the $1/4$ disk of the first quadrant (area $\pi k^2/4$), so the number of orbitals is $\dfrac{\pi k^2/4}{(\pi/L)^2}=\dfrac{L^2k^2}{4\pi}$. Multiplying by the $2$ spin states gives $N(\varepsilon)=\dfrac{L^2k^2}{2\pi}$. Substituting $k^2=2m\varepsilon/\hbar^2$ gives $N(\varepsilon)=\dfrac{mL^2}{\pi\hbar^2}\varepsilon$, a linear function of $\varepsilon$. Differentiating gives $g(\varepsilon)=\dfrac{\dd N}{\dd\varepsilon}=\dfrac{mL^2}{\pi\hbar^2}$ (constant).

(2) At $T=0$, $N=\int_0^{E_{\mathrm F}}g\dd\varepsilon=gE_{\mathrm F}$. From $N=n_2L^2$, $n_2L^2=\dfrac{mL^2}{\pi\hbar^2}E_{\mathrm F}$, that is, $E_{\mathrm F}=\dfrac{\pi\hbar^2n_2}m$ (here $k_{\mathrm F}^2=2\pi n_2$).

(3) Let $z=e^{\mu/k_{\mathrm B}T}$ and $y=e^{-\varepsilon/k_{\mathrm B}T}$. Then $f=\dfrac1{e^{(\varepsilon-\mu)/k_{\mathrm B}T}+1}=\dfrac{zy}{1+zy}$ (multiplying the numerator and denominator by $y$), and $\dd\varepsilon=-k_{\mathrm B}T\,\dd y/y$. Since $\varepsilon:0\to\infty$ corresponds to $y:1\to0$,

$$ \int_0^\infty f\dd\varepsilon=k_{\mathrm B}T\int_0^1\frac{z}{1+zy}\dd y=k_{\mathrm B}T\bigl[\ln(1+zy)\bigr]_0^1=k_{\mathrm B}T\ln\bigl(1+e^{\mu/k_{\mathrm B}T}\bigr) $$

From $N=g\int f\dd\varepsilon=gE_{\mathrm F}$ we get $k_{\mathrm B}T\ln(1+e^{\mu/k_{\mathrm B}T})=E_{\mathrm F}$. Solving $e^{\mu/k_{\mathrm B}T}=e^{E_{\mathrm F}/k_{\mathrm B}T}-1$ gives $\mu=k_{\mathrm B}T\ln\bigl(e^{E_{\mathrm F}/k_{\mathrm B}T}-1\bigr)$. At low temperatures, $\mu=E_{\mathrm F}+k_{\mathrm B}T\ln(1-e^{-E_{\mathrm F}/k_{\mathrm B}T})\approx E_{\mathrm F}-k_{\mathrm B}Te^{-E_{\mathrm F}/k_{\mathrm B}T}$, and unlike the three-dimensional case (a shift proportional to $T^2$, Section 52.9), the shift is exponentially small (because the density of states is constant, the $T^2$ correction to $\mu$ vanishes since $g'(E_{\mathrm F})=0$). For example, for $k_{\mathrm B}T=0.5E_{\mathrm F}$, $\mu=0.927E_{\mathrm F}$, and for $k_{\mathrm B}T=E_{\mathrm F}$, $\mu=0.541E_{\mathrm F}$.

(4) $n_2=3\times10^{11}\ \mathrm{cm^{-2}}=3\times10^{15}\ \mathrm{m^{-2}}$ and $m^*=0.067\times9.109\times10^{-31}=6.10\times10^{-32}\ \mathrm{kg}$. $E_{\mathrm F}=\pi\hbar^2n_2/m^*=\pi\times(1.0546\times10^{-34})^2\times3\times10^{15}/6.10\times10^{-32}\ \mathrm J=1.72\times10^{-21}\ \mathrm J=10.7\ \mathrm{meV}$. $T_{\mathrm F}=E_{\mathrm F}/k_{\mathrm B}=1.72\times10^{-21}/1.381\times10^{-23}=124\ \mathrm K$. Therefore the gas is strongly degenerate at liquid-helium temperature ($4.2\ \mathrm K$), whereas at room temperature ($300\ \mathrm K>T_{\mathrm F}$) it is no longer degenerate.

Exercise 52.7: Electronic specific heat of aluminum

Solution

(1) The number density of atoms is $\dfrac{2.70\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{kg/m^3}}{26.98\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{kg/mol}}\times6.022\times10^{23}\ \mathrm{mol^{-1}}=6.03\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$. Multiplying by the number of valence electrons, $3$, gives the electron number density $n=1.81\times10^{29}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$.

(2) $k_{\mathrm F}=(3\pi^2n)^{1/3}=(3\times9.870\times1.808\times10^{29})^{1/3}=(5.353\times10^{30})^{1/3}=1.749\times10^{10}\ \mathrm{m^{-1}}$. $E_{\mathrm F}=\dfrac{\hbar^2k_{\mathrm F}^2}{2m}=\dfrac{(1.0546\times10^{-34})^2\times(1.749\times10^{10})^2}{2\times9.109\times10^{-31}}=1.867\times10^{-18}\ \mathrm J=11.7\ \mathrm{eV}$. $T_{\mathrm F}=E_{\mathrm F}/k_{\mathrm B}=1.867\times10^{-18}/1.381\times10^{-23}=1.35\times10^5\ \mathrm K$.

(3) For $1\ \mathrm{mol}$ of atoms, $N=3N_{\mathrm A}$, so $Nk_{\mathrm B}=3R$. $\gamma=\dfrac{\pi^2}2\dfrac{3R}{T_{\mathrm F}}=4.935\times\dfrac{3\times8.314}{1.353\times10^5}=9.10\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K^2)}=0.91\ \mathrm{mJ/(mol\,K^2)}$. The electronic specific heat at $T=300\ \mathrm K$ is $\gamma T=0.273\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)}$. This is $0.273/37.4=0.73\,\%$ of the classical value $\tfrac32\times3R=37.4\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)}$. It is smaller than for copper ($1.2\,\%$, Example 52.16) because aluminum has a higher $T_{\mathrm F}$ (a higher electron density), so $T/T_{\mathrm F}$ is smaller and the degeneracy is stronger.

Exercise 52.8: Comparing $T_{\mathrm F}$ of a Fermi gas with $T_{\mathrm c}$ of a Bose gas

Solution

Taking the ratio of the two formulas in the hint, $m,n,\hbar,k_{\mathrm B}$ cancel and we obtain

$$ \frac{T_{\mathrm c}}{T_{\mathrm F}}=\frac{2\pi\,\zeta(\tfrac32)^{-2/3}}{(3\pi^2)^{2/3}/2}=\frac{4\pi}{\bigl(3\pi^2\zeta(\tfrac32)\bigr)^{2/3}} $$

Numerically, $3\pi^2\zeta(\tfrac32)=3\times9.8696\times2.6124=77.35$, $77.35^{2/3}=18.15$ ($77.35^{1/3}=4.26$, squared), and $4\pi=12.57$, so $T_{\mathrm c}/T_{\mathrm F}=12.57/18.15=0.692$.

For helium-3 ($m=3.016\times1.6605\times10^{-27}=5.008\times10^{-27}\ \mathrm{kg}$) with $n=1.6\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$, $(3\pi^2n)^{2/3}=(4.738\times10^{29})^{2/3}=6.08\times10^{19}\ \mathrm{m^{-2}}$ and $\dfrac{\hbar^2}{2mk_{\mathrm B}}=\dfrac{(1.0546\times10^{-34})^2}{2\times5.008\times10^{-27}\times1.381\times10^{-23}}=8.04\times10^{-20}\ \mathrm{m^2\,K}$, so $T_{\mathrm F}=8.04\times10^{-20}\times6.08\times10^{19}\ \mathrm K=4.9\ \mathrm K$ (an estimate that treats it as a free Fermi gas).

Conclusion: for the same mass and density, the degeneracy temperature $T_{\mathrm F}$ of a Fermi gas and the condensation temperature $T_{\mathrm c}$ of a Bose gas are of the same order of magnitude ($T_{\mathrm c}\approx0.7\,T_{\mathrm F}$), because in both cases degeneracy begins at the temperature at which “the thermal de Broglie wavelength $\lambda_{\mathrm T}$ becomes comparable to the interparticle distance $n^{-1/3}$” ($n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^3\sim1$). The temperature at which degeneracy begins is determined independently of the type of particle, and the difference between the types shows up in the behavior after degeneracy sets in (fermions: a Fermi sphere with a step-function distribution; bosons: condensation).

Exercise 52.9: When the reservoir is an ideal gas, check $\beta$ and $\mu$

Solution

(1) Abbreviate $N_R,V_R,E_R$ as $N,V,E$. Then $\ln\Omega_R=S_R/k_{\mathrm B}=N\bigl[\ln V+\tfrac32\ln E-\tfrac52\ln N+\tfrac32\ln\tfrac{4\pi m}{3h^2}+\tfrac52\bigr]$ (we used $\ln(V/N)=\ln V-\ln N$ and $\tfrac32\ln\tfrac{4\pi mE}{3Nh^2}=\tfrac32\ln E-\tfrac32\ln N+\tfrac32\ln\tfrac{4\pi m}{3h^2}$). Differentiating with respect to $E$ gives

$$ \left(\pdiff{\ln\Omega_R}{E}\right)_{N}=\frac{3N}{2E} $$

Differentiating with respect to $N$, by the product rule we get the contents of $[\ ]$ itself plus $N\times\bigl(-\tfrac5{2N}\bigr)=-\tfrac52$, and the latter cancels the $+\tfrac52$, so that

$$ \left(\pdiff{\ln\Omega_R}{N}\right)_{E}=\ln V+\frac32\ln E-\frac52\ln N+\frac32\ln\frac{4\pi m}{3h^2}=\ln\left\{\frac VN\left(\frac{4\pi mE}{3Nh^2}\right)^{3/2}\right\} $$

(2) Substituting $E=\tfrac32Nk_{\mathrm B}T$, the first equation becomes $\dfrac{3N}{2\cdot\frac32Nk_{\mathrm B}T}=\dfrac1{k_{\mathrm B}T}=\beta$. In the second equation, $\dfrac{4\pi mE}{3Nh^2}=\dfrac{4\pi m\cdot\frac32Nk_{\mathrm B}T}{3Nh^2}=\dfrac{2\pi mk_{\mathrm B}T}{h^2}=\dfrac1{\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2}$, so the argument of the logarithm is $\dfrac VN\dfrac1{\lambda_{\mathrm T}^3}=\dfrac1{n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^3}$, and the second equation is therefore $-\ln(n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^3)=-\beta\mu$ ($\mu=k_{\mathrm B}T\ln(n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^3)$). The first-order term of the Taylor expansion in Section 52.1 is $-E_{j,N}\times\beta-N\times(-\beta\mu)=-\beta(E_{j,N}-\mu N)$, so we obtain $P_{j,N}\propto\Omega_R\propto\exp\bigl[-\beta(E_{j,N}-\mu N)\bigr]$.

(3) If $E_{j,N}=0$ and $N=1$, the change in $\ln\Omega_R$ is $-1\times(-\beta\mu)=\beta\mu$, so the number of states of the reservoir is multiplied by $e^{\beta\mu}=n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^3$. For helium-4 gas ($300\ \mathrm K$, $1\ \mathrm{atm}$), the value in Table 52.3 is $n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^3=3.1\times10^{-6}$ ($\mu=-12.7\,k_{\mathrm B}T$). The number of states of the reservoir is multiplied by about $3\times10^{-6}$; that is, it is reduced to about one part in 320,000 ($32$ × 10,000). When the reservoir gives up one particle, the states (places) available to it decrease by that much, so a state in which system $s$ contains a particle is harder to realize than the empty state.

Exercise 52.10: Grand partition function of a system of fermions with two orbitals

Solution

(1) There are four pairs of occupation numbers $(n_1,n_2)$ ($n_i=0,1$). $(0,0)$: $N=0$, $E=0$, weight $1$. $(1,0)$: $N=1$, $E=0$, weight $z$. $(0,1)$: $N=1$, $E=\varepsilon$, weight $zx$. $(1,1)$: $N=2$, $E=\varepsilon$, weight $z^2x$ ($e^{-\beta(E-\mu N)}=e^{-\beta\varepsilon}e^{2\beta\mu}$). Therefore the grand partition function factorizes as

$$ \Xi=1+z+zx+z^2x=(1+z)+zx(1+z)=(1+z)(1+zx) $$

This is the product $\Xi=\Xi_1\Xi_2$ of the grand partition functions of the individual single-particle states, $\Xi_1=1+z$ (energy $0$) and $\Xi_2=1+zx=1+e^{-\beta(\varepsilon-\mu)}$; this corresponds to $\Xi=\prod_i\Xi_i$ in Section 52.4.

(2) $\ln\Xi=\ln(1+z)+\ln(1+zx)$. Since $\partial/\partial\mu=\beta z\,\partial/\partial z$, $\langle N\rangle=k_{\mathrm B}T\,\partial\ln\Xi/\partial\mu=z\,\partial\ln\Xi/\partial z=\dfrac z{1+z}+\dfrac{zx}{1+zx}$. Dividing the numerator and denominator by $z$ (by $zx$ for the second term) gives $\dfrac1{z^{-1}+1}+\dfrac1{(zx)^{-1}+1}$. Since $z^{-1}=e^{-\beta\mu}=e^{\beta(0-\mu)}$ and $(zx)^{-1}=e^{\beta(\varepsilon-\mu)}$, this is exactly $f(0)+f(\varepsilon)$.

(3) For $k_{\mathrm B}T=\varepsilon$, $\beta\varepsilon=1$, and for $\mu=\varepsilon/2$, $z=e^{1/2}=1.6487$, $x=e^{-1}=0.36788$, and $zx=0.60653$. $f(0)=z/(1+z)=1.6487/2.6487=0.6225$ and $f(\varepsilon)=zx/(1+zx)=0.60653/1.60653=0.3775$. The total is $\langle N\rangle=0.6225+0.3775=1.0000$. It comes out to exactly $1$ because $\mu=\varepsilon/2$ lies midway between the two levels, so the symmetry $f(\mu+\delta)+f(\mu-\delta)=1$ (Section 52.5, with $\delta=\varepsilon/2$) can be used. The probability that both levels are occupied is obtained by dividing the weight of the state $(1,1)$, $z^2x=e\times e^{-1}=1.0000$, by $\Xi=(1+z)(1+zx)=2.6487\times1.6065=4.2552$, which gives $0.2350$. This agrees with $f(0)f(\varepsilon)=0.6225\times0.3775=0.2350$, which shows that the two levels are independent.

Exercise 52.11: $\Xi$, $\mu$, and pressure of a two-dimensional ideal gas

Solution

(1) By the same procedure as in Example 52.2, $\Xi=\sum_Nz^NZ_N=\sum_N\dfrac{(zZ_1)^N}{N!}=e^{zZ_1}=\exp\bigl(zS/\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2\bigr)$ ($z=e^{\beta\mu}$).

(2) $\ln\Xi=zS/\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2$. Since $\partial z/\partial\mu=\beta z$, $N=k_{\mathrm B}T\,\partial\ln\Xi/\partial\mu=k_{\mathrm B}T\cdot\beta zS/\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2=zS/\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2$. Therefore $z=e^{\beta\mu}=N\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2/S=n_2\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2$ and $\mu=k_{\mathrm B}T\ln(n_2\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2)$.

(3) $J=-k_{\mathrm B}T\ln\Xi=-k_{\mathrm B}T\,zS/\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2=-k_{\mathrm B}TN$. On the other hand, $J=-\Pi S$, so $\Pi S=Nk_{\mathrm B}T$, that is, $\Pi=n_2k_{\mathrm B}T$ (the equation of state in two dimensions). Since $J$ is proportional to $S$ when $T,\mu$ are fixed, the same result follows from $\Pi=-(\partial J/\partial S)_{T,\mu}=k_{\mathrm B}Tz/\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2=n_2k_{\mathrm B}T$. As for the units, $n_2k_{\mathrm B}T$ has units $\mathrm{m^{-2}\cdot J}=\mathrm{N/m}$, which agrees with a force per unit length.

(4) $m=39.948\times1.6605\times10^{-27}=6.634\times10^{-26}\ \mathrm{kg}$ and $\lambda_{\mathrm T}=1.595\times10^{-11}\ \mathrm m$ (the same as in Exercise 52.1). $n_2\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2=10^{16}\times(1.595\times10^{-11})^2=2.543\times10^{-6}$. Since $\ln(2.543\times10^{-6})=-12.88$, $\mu=-12.88\,k_{\mathrm B}T=-12.88\times0.02585\ \mathrm{eV}=-0.333\ \mathrm{eV}$. The surface pressure is $\Pi=n_2k_{\mathrm B}T=10^{16}\times4.142\times10^{-21}\ \mathrm{N/m}=4.14\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{N/m}$ ($0.041\ \mathrm{mN/m}$).

Exercise 52.12: How the Fermi distribution becomes a step function at low temperature

Solution

(1) With $\beta=1/k_{\mathrm B}T$, the exponent is $\beta(\varepsilon-\mu)$. If $\varepsilon\lt\mu$, then $\varepsilon-\mu\lt0$, so as $\beta\to\infty$, $\beta(\varepsilon-\mu)\to-\infty$ and $e^{\beta(\varepsilon-\mu)}\to0$, and therefore $f\to1/(0+1)=1$. If $\varepsilon\gt\mu$, then $\beta(\varepsilon-\mu)\to+\infty$, $e^{\beta(\varepsilon-\mu)}\to\infty$, and $f\to0$. At $\varepsilon=\mu$, the exponent is $0$ at any $T$, so $e^0=1$ and $f=\tfrac12$ (the midpoint of the jump of the step).

(2) Compute $x=(\varepsilon/\mu-1)/(k_{\mathrm B}T/\mu)$ and substitute it into $f=1/(e^x+1)$.

$k_{\mathrm B}T/\mu$$\varepsilon/\mu=0.9$$0.99$$1.01$$1.1$
$0.1$ ($x=-1,\,-0.1,\,+0.1,\,+1$)$0.7311$$0.5250$$0.4750$$0.2689$
$0.01$ ($x=-10,\,-1,\,+1,\,+10$)$0.99995$$0.7311$$0.2689$$4.5\times10^{-5}$

If the temperature is reduced to $1/10$, the values at the same distance from $\mu$ ($\varepsilon/\mu=0.9$ or $1.1$) stick to $1$ and $0$: $0.73\to0.99995$ and $0.27\to4.5\times10^{-5}$. The width of the transition (from $f=0.9$ to $0.1$; $4.4\,k_{\mathrm B}T$) also becomes $1/10$, and as $T\to0$ the distribution becomes a step function. Moreover, $f(\mu+\delta)+f(\mu-\delta)=1$ (for example, $0.7311+0.2689=1$) holds for every pair of columns in the table.

Exercise 52.13: Why does condensation not occur in a two-dimensional ideal Bose gas?

Solution

(1) The mean number of particles is $N=\int_0^\infty g(\varepsilon)\langle n_\varepsilon\rangle\dd\varepsilon$ ($\langle n_\varepsilon\rangle=1/(e^{(\varepsilon-\mu)/k_{\mathrm B}T}-1)$). Changing the variable to $x=\varepsilon/k_{\mathrm B}T$ and using the integral given in the Hint, we obtain

$$ N=\frac{Am}{2\pi\hbar^2}\,k_{\mathrm B}T\int_0^\infty\frac{\dd x}{z^{-1}e^{x}-1}=\frac{Amk_{\mathrm B}T}{2\pi\hbar^2}\bigl[-\ln(1-z)\bigr] $$

Since $\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2=2\pi\hbar^2/(mk_{\mathrm B}T)$ (using $h=2\pi\hbar$, this is the same as $\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2=h^2/(2\pi mk_{\mathrm B}T)$), it follows that

$$ n=\frac NA=\frac{-\ln(1-z)}{\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2} $$

(The integral given in the Hint is evaluated by noting that $\dfrac{1}{z^{-1}e^x-1}=\dfrac{ze^{-x}}{1-ze^{-x}}$ is equal to $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd x}\ln(1-ze^{-x})$, so that $\bigl[\ln(1-ze^{-x})\bigr]_0^\infty=0-\ln(1-z)$.)

(2) From $-\ln(1-z)=n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2$ we obtain

$$ z=1-\exp\left(-n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2\right) $$

For $n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2\ll1$ we have $z\approx n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2$ (the same form as the result for a classical ideal gas).

(3) For any finite values of $n$ and $T$, $n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2$ is finite, so $z=1-e^{-n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2}\lt1$, that is, $\mu\lt0$. The condition $z\to1$ ($\mu\to0$) is reached only when $n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2\to\infty$, that is, in the limit $T\to0$. In three dimensions, even when $\mu\to0$, the upper limit $\int g(\varepsilon)/(e^{\varepsilon/k_{\mathrm B}T}-1)\dd\varepsilon$ on the number of particles that can enter the excited states is finite ($\propto\zeta(3/2)$), so the particles in excess of it fall into the ground state (condensation). In two dimensions, the density of states is constant, and $\int\dd\varepsilon/\varepsilon$ diverges logarithmically as $\varepsilon\to0$, so as $\mu\to0$ the excited states alone can accommodate any number of particles. Therefore, at any density the excited states can take in all the particles, and a macroscopic number of particles collecting in the ground state at a finite temperature (condensation) does not occur.

(4) For $n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2=1$, $z=1-e^{-1}=1-0.3679=0.632$ and $\mu/k_{\mathrm B}T=\ln z=-0.459$. Substituting the same $n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2=1$ into the classical ideal-gas formula $z=n\lambda_{\mathrm T}^2$ would give $z=1$ ($\mu=0$), so for the Bose distribution $z$ is smaller than in the classical approximation ($\mu$ is lower).

← Back to Chapter 52, “The Grand Canonical Distribution and Quantum Statistics — The Fermi and Bose Distributions”

Chapter 53 Statistics of Photons and Phonons — Black-Body Radiation and the Debye Model

Exercise 53.1: The photon's $\mu=0$ and the mean number of photons

Solution

(a) The chemical potential is $\mu=(\partial A/\partial N)_{T,V}$, that is, the change in the free energy $A$ when one particle is added at constant temperature and volume. For an ordinary gas the number of particles $N$ is given from outside, so $\mu$ is in general not $0$. However, the walls of the cavity freely absorb and emit photons, so the number of photons $N$ is not conserved, and in thermal equilibrium $N$ settles by itself so that the free energy is minimized. At the point where $A(T,V,N)$ is minimized as a function of $N$, $\partial A/\partial N=0$. This directly means $\mu=0$. Looking at it from another viewpoint, the partition function of a single mode, $\sum_{n=0}^\infty e^{-\beta\hbar\omega n}$, is a sum taken with no restriction whatsoever on the photon number $n$, and it has the form of the factor $e^{\beta\mu n}$ of the grand canonical distribution with $\mu=0$.

(b) The mean number of photons is $\langle n\rangle=1/(e^x-1)$, and the mean energy is $h\nu\langle n\rangle=k_{\mathrm B}T\cdot x/(e^x-1)$ ($x=h\nu/k_{\mathrm B}T$).

For $x=1$, with $e^1-1=1.71828$, we have $\langle n\rangle=0.5820$, and the mean energy is $1\times0.5820\,k_{\mathrm B}T=0.582\,k_{\mathrm B}T$.

For $x=5$, with $e^5-1=147.413$, we have $\langle n\rangle=6.78\times10^{-3}$, and the mean energy is $5\times6.78\times10^{-3}\,k_{\mathrm B}T=0.0339\,k_{\mathrm B}T$.

Compared with the equipartition value $k_{\mathrm B}T$, this is only about $58\%$ at $x=1$ and about $3.4\%$ at $x=5$. As $x\to0$, $x/(e^x-1)\to1$, which agrees with the equipartition value, so it has been confirmed that only the modes with $h\nu\ll k_{\mathrm B}T$ behave classically, while the modes with large $h\nu$ are frozen out.

Exercise 53.2: The wavelength form of Planck's formula and the difference between the peaks

Solution

(a) Substitute $\nu=c/\lambda$ into Planck's formula $u(\nu,T)\,\dd\nu=\dfrac{8\pi h\nu^3}{c^3}\dfrac{1}{e^{h\nu/k_{\mathrm B}T}-1}\dd\nu$. Since $\nu^3=c^3/\lambda^3$ and $\abs{\dd\nu}=(c/\lambda^2)\,\dd\lambda$ (differentiating $\nu=c/\lambda$ with respect to $\lambda$ gives $-c/\lambda^2$), we have

$$ \frac{8\pi h}{c^3}\cdot\frac{c^3}{\lambda^3}\cdot\frac{c}{\lambda^2}\,\dd\lambda=\frac{8\pi hc}{\lambda^5}\,\dd\lambda,\qquad\frac{h\nu}{k_{\mathrm B}T}=\frac{hc}{\lambda k_{\mathrm B}T} $$

Therefore $u(\lambda,T)\,\dd\lambda=\dfrac{8\pi hc}{\lambda^5}\dfrac{1}{e^{hc/(\lambda k_{\mathrm B}T)}-1}\dd\lambda$.

(b) The condition for $u(\nu,T)$ to be at its maximum is $x=3(1-e^{-x})$ ($x=h\nu/k_{\mathrm B}T$), and the solution is $x_\nu=2.8214$. Hence

$$ \nu_{\max}=\frac{x_\nu k_{\mathrm B}T}{h}=\frac{2.8214\times1.380649\times10^{-23}\times5772}{6.62607\times10^{-34}}=3.393\times10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz} $$

The corresponding wavelength is $c/\nu_{\max}=2.998\times10^{8}/3.393\times10^{14}=8.83\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm m=883\ \mathrm{nm}$ (near infrared). On the other hand, the peak of $u(\lambda,T)$, from the displacement law, is at $\lambda_{\max}=b/T=2.898\times10^{-3}/5772=5.02\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm m=502\ \mathrm{nm}$ (visible light). The values $883\ \mathrm{nm}$ and $502\ \mathrm{nm}$ do not coincide. Their ratio is $883/502=1.76$, which agrees with $x_\lambda/x_\nu=4.965/2.821=1.760$. Even for the same spectrum, the position of the peak changes depending on whether it is viewed per unit frequency or per unit wavelength, because of the Jacobian $\dd\nu=(c/\lambda^2)\,\dd\lambda$.

Exercise 53.3: The Stefan–Boltzmann constant and how to use it

Solution

(a) Let $x=h\nu/(k_{\mathrm B}T)$. Then $\nu=(k_{\mathrm B}T/h)x$, $\dd\nu=(k_{\mathrm B}T/h)\dd x$, and the range of integration remains $0\to\infty$.

$$ u(T)=\frac{8\pi h}{c^3}\int_0^\infty\frac{\nu^3\,\dd\nu}{e^{h\nu/k_{\mathrm B}T}-1}=\frac{8\pi h}{c^3}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{h}\right)^4\int_0^\infty\frac{x^3}{e^x-1}\dd x=\frac{8\pi(k_{\mathrm B}T)^4}{h^3c^3}\cdot\Gamma(4)\zeta(4) $$

By Formula 47.4 of Chapter 47 ($p=3$), $\Gamma(4)\zeta(4)=3!\times\pi^4/90=\pi^4/15$. Hence $u(T)=\dfrac{8\pi^5k_{\mathrm B}^4}{15h^3c^3}T^4=aT^4$. The reason $T^4$ comes out of the integral is that $(k_{\mathrm B}T/h)^3\cdot(k_{\mathrm B}T/h)$ comes out of $\nu^3\,\dd\nu$, and the remaining integral over $x$ is a number independent of the temperature.

(b) $\sigma=\dfrac c4a=\dfrac c4\cdot\dfrac{8\pi^5k_{\mathrm B}^4}{15h^3c^3}=\dfrac{2\pi^5k_{\mathrm B}^4}{15h^3c^2}$. Numerically, with $a=7.566\times10^{-16}\ \mathrm{J\,m^{-3}K^{-4}}$ and $c/4=7.4948\times10^{7}\ \mathrm{m/s}$, we get $\sigma=7.566\times10^{-16}\times7.4948\times10^{7}=5.670\times10^{-8}\ \mathrm{W\,m^{-2}K^{-4}}$.

(c) $j=\sigma T^4=5.670\times10^{-8}\times(300)^4=5.670\times10^{-8}\times8.1\times10^{9}=459\ \mathrm{W/m^2}$. That is, about $459\ \mathrm W$ per $1\ \mathrm{m^2}$. If the temperature rises by $10\%$, then $T\to1.1\,T$ and $j\to1.1^4j=1.4641\,j$, that is, about $1.46$ times as large (a $46\%$ increase), or about $672\ \mathrm{W/m^2}$. A slight rise in temperature greatly increases the radiated power.

Exercise 53.4: The number density of photons and the mean energy per photon

Solution

The number of photons per unit volume is obtained by summing “the number of modes” × “the mean number of photons per mode” and dividing by the volume:

$$ n_\gamma=\frac1V\int_0^\infty g(\omega)\langle n\rangle\dd\omega=\frac{1}{\pi^2c^3}\int_0^\infty\frac{\omega^2\,\dd\omega}{e^{\hbar\omega/k_{\mathrm B}T}-1} $$

Here we used $g(\omega)=V\omega^2/(\pi^2c^3)$ and $\langle n\rangle=1/(e^{\hbar\omega/k_{\mathrm B}T}-1)$. Setting $x=\hbar\omega/k_{\mathrm B}T$, we have $\omega^2\dd\omega=(k_{\mathrm B}T/\hbar)^3x^2\dd x$, and by Formula 47.4 ($p=2$), $\int_0^\infty x^2/(e^x-1)\,\dd x=\Gamma(3)\zeta(3)=2\zeta(3)$, so

$$ n_\gamma=\frac{1}{\pi^2c^3}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{\hbar}\right)^3\cdot2\zeta(3)=\frac{2\zeta(3)}{\pi^2}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{\hbar c}\right)^3 $$

The coefficient is $2\zeta(3)/\pi^2=2\times1.2021/9.8696=0.2436$.

(a) $T=300\ \mathrm K$: $k_{\mathrm B}T=4.142\times10^{-21}\ \mathrm J$, $\hbar c=1.0546\times10^{-34}\times2.998\times10^{8}=3.1615\times10^{-26}\ \mathrm{J\,m}$, $k_{\mathrm B}T/(\hbar c)=1.310\times10^{5}\ \mathrm{m^{-1}}$, and its cube is $2.249\times10^{15}$, so $n_\gamma=0.2436\times2.249\times10^{15}=5.5\times10^{14}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$.

(b) $T=5772\ \mathrm K$: $k_{\mathrm B}T=7.969\times10^{-20}\ \mathrm J$, $k_{\mathrm B}T/(\hbar c)=2.521\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{m^{-1}}$, and the cube is $1.602\times10^{19}$, so $n_\gamma=0.2436\times1.602\times10^{19}=3.9\times10^{18}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$.

(c) Since $u=aT^4=\dfrac{\pi^2(k_{\mathrm B}T)^4}{15\hbar^3c^3}$ (we rewrote $a=8\pi^5k_{\mathrm B}^4/(15h^3c^3)$ using $h=2\pi\hbar$),

$$ \frac{u}{n_\gamma}=\frac{\pi^2(k_{\mathrm B}T)^4}{15\hbar^3c^3}\cdot\frac{\pi^2}{2\zeta(3)}\frac{(\hbar c)^3}{(k_{\mathrm B}T)^3}=\frac{\pi^4}{30\,\zeta(3)}k_{\mathrm B}T=\frac{97.409}{30\times1.2021}k_{\mathrm B}T=2.70\,k_{\mathrm B}T $$

The mean energy per photon is about $2.7$ times $k_{\mathrm B}T$, independent of the temperature.

Exercise 53.5: The density of states and the Debye temperature in the Debye model

Solution

(a) From $\displaystyle\int_0^{\omega_{\mathrm D}}C\omega^2\,\dd\omega=\dfrac{C\omega_{\mathrm D}^3}{3}=3N$ we get $C=\dfrac{9N}{\omega_{\mathrm D}^3}$. Hence $g(\omega)=9N\omega^2/\omega_{\mathrm D}^3$ ($0\le\omega\le\omega_{\mathrm D}$).

(b) Using $c_{\mathrm l}=6.0\ \mathrm{km/s}$ and $c_{\mathrm t}=3.0\ \mathrm{km/s}$ in units of $\mathrm{km/s}$, we have

$$ \frac{3}{c_s^3}=\frac1{6.0^3}+\frac{2}{3.0^3}=4.630\times10^{-3}+7.407\times10^{-2}=7.870\times10^{-2}\ (\mathrm{km/s})^{-3} $$

so $c_s^3=38.12\ (\mathrm{km/s})^3$ and $c_s=3.365\ \mathrm{km/s}=3.365\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{m/s}$. The upper limit of the wavenumber is $k_{\mathrm D}=(6\pi^2n)^{1/3}=(59.22\times6.0\times10^{28})^{1/3}=(3.553\times10^{30})^{1/3}=1.526\times10^{10}\ \mathrm{m^{-1}}$. Therefore

$$ \omega_{\mathrm D}=c_sk_{\mathrm D}=3.365\times10^{3}\times1.526\times10^{10}=5.14\times10^{13}\ \mathrm{rad/s} $$ $$ \theta_{\mathrm D}=\frac{\hbar\omega_{\mathrm D}}{k_{\mathrm B}}=\frac{1.0546\times10^{-34}\times5.14\times10^{13}}{1.3806\times10^{-23}}=3.9\times10^{2}\ \mathrm K $$

(about $392\ \mathrm K$). The shortest wavelength is $2\pi/k_{\mathrm D}=2\pi/(1.526\times10^{10})=4.1\times10^{-10}\ \mathrm m=0.41\ \mathrm{nm}$, which is about 1.6 times the interatomic spacing $n^{-1/3}=0.26\ \mathrm{nm}$.

Exercise 53.6: Deriving Debye's $T^3$ law

Solution

(a) We have $U(T)=9Nk_{\mathrm B}T\,(T/\theta_{\mathrm D})^3\int_0^{\theta_{\mathrm D}/T}x^3/(e^x-1)\,\dd x$, and since $T\ll\theta_{\mathrm D}$, we replace the upper limit by $\infty$. The integrand becomes small for large $x$, like $x^3e^{-x}$, so the error is negligible (about $1\%$ at $\theta_{\mathrm D}/T=10$). From Formula 47.4 ($p=3$) the integral is $\pi^4/15$, and

$$ U(T)=9Nk_{\mathrm B}T\left(\frac{T}{\theta_{\mathrm D}}\right)^3\frac{\pi^4}{15}=\frac{3\pi^4}{5}\,\frac{Nk_{\mathrm B}}{\theta_{\mathrm D}^3}\,T^4 $$

(since $9/15=3/5$). Differentiating with respect to $T$, we obtain $C_V=\dfrac{\dd U}{\dd T}=\dfrac{12\pi^4}{5}\dfrac{Nk_{\mathrm B}}{\theta_{\mathrm D}^3}T^3=\dfrac{12\pi^4}{5}Nk_{\mathrm B}\left(\dfrac T{\theta_{\mathrm D}}\right)^3$.

(b) For one mole, $Nk_{\mathrm B}=R$. Since $12\pi^4/5=233.78$, we have $\dfrac{12\pi^4}{5}R=233.78\times8.314=1944\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)}$. With $T/\theta_{\mathrm D}=4/400=0.01$,

$$ C_V=1944\times(0.01)^3=1.94\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{J/(mol\,K)} $$

The quantity of heat required for heating is the increase in internal energy from $0\ \mathrm K$ to $4\ \mathrm K$, $\Delta U=U(4\ \mathrm K)$; substituting into $U=\frac{3\pi^4}{5}RT^4/\theta_{\mathrm D}^3$ gives

$$ \Delta U=58.44\times8.314\times\frac{(4)^4}{(400)^3}=485.9\times\frac{256}{6.4\times10^{7}}=1.94\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{J/mol} $$

(with $\frac{3\pi^4}{5}=58.44$). From the relation between $U\propto T^4$ and $C_V\propto T^3$, we have $U=C_VT/4=1.94\times10^{-3}\times4/4$, which agrees. Only about $2\ \mathrm{mJ}$ of heat is enough to warm $1\ \mathrm{mol}$ to $4\ \mathrm K$.

Exercise 53.7: High-temperature expansion of the Debye model

Solution

We have $C_V=9Nk_{\mathrm B}y^{-3}\int_0^y x^4e^x/(e^x-1)^2\,\dd x$ ($y=\theta_{\mathrm D}/T$). First we expand $x^2e^x/(e^x-1)^2$. Since $e^x/(e^x-1)^2=1/(4\sinh^2(x/2))$ and $\sinh(x/2)=\frac x2\left(1+\frac{x^2}{24}+\cdots\right)$, we have $\sinh^2(x/2)=\frac{x^2}{4}\left(1+\frac{x^2}{12}+\cdots\right)$. Therefore

$$ \frac{x^2e^x}{(e^x-1)^2}=\frac{x^2}{4\sinh^2(x/2)}=\frac{1}{1+x^2/12+\cdots}=1-\frac{x^2}{12}+\cdots $$

The integrand is $x^4e^x/(e^x-1)^2=x^2\cdot\dfrac{x^2e^x}{(e^x-1)^2}=x^2-\dfrac{x^4}{12}+\cdots$, and integrating gives $\displaystyle\int_0^y\left(x^2-\frac{x^4}{12}\right)\dd x=\frac{y^3}{3}-\frac{y^5}{60}$. Hence

$$ C_V=9Nk_{\mathrm B}y^{-3}\left(\frac{y^3}{3}-\frac{y^5}{60}\right)=3Nk_{\mathrm B}\left(1-\frac{y^2}{20}\right)=3Nk_{\mathrm B}\left[1-\frac{1}{20}\left(\frac{\theta_{\mathrm D}}{T}\right)^2\right] $$

Numerical comparison: at $T=\theta_{\mathrm D}$ ($y=1$), the approximate value is $1-1/20=0.9500$, and the value by numerical integration is $0.9517$ (error $0.2\%$). At $T=2\theta_{\mathrm D}$ ($y=0.5$), the approximate value is $1-0.25/20=0.9875$, and the value by numerical integration is $0.9876$ (error $0.01\%$). Adding the next term $+y^4/560$ gives $0.9518$ at $y=1$, which nearly agrees with the value by numerical integration. It has been confirmed that the approximation is better at higher temperatures.

Exercise 53.8: The one-dimensional Debye model (specific heat of a chain-like material)

Solution

(a) If we impose periodic boundary conditions on a chain of length $L$, the wavenumbers are $k=2\pi j/L$ ($j$ is an integer), and the lattice points in the (one-dimensional) wavenumber space lie on a line at intervals of $2\pi/L$. The number of lattice points whose wavenumber magnitude is at most $K$ is the length $2K$ of the range $-K\le k\le K$ divided by the interval $2\pi/L$, namely $KL/\pi$; since there is only one polarization, the number of modes up to $\omega$ is $N(\omega)=L\omega/(\pi c_s)$. Therefore $g(\omega)=\dd N/\dd\omega=L/(\pi c_s)$ (constant). From the normalization condition $\int_0^{\omega_{\mathrm D}}g\,\dd\omega=N$ we get $L\omega_{\mathrm D}/(\pi c_s)=N$, that is, $\omega_{\mathrm D}=\pi c_sN/L=\pi c_s/a$ ($a=L/N$ is the interatomic spacing), so that

$$ g(\omega)=\frac{N}{\omega_{\mathrm D}}\quad(0\le\omega\le\omega_{\mathrm D}) $$

holds.

(b) $U=\displaystyle\int_0^{\omega_{\mathrm D}}\frac{N}{\omega_{\mathrm D}}\,\frac{\hbar\omega}{e^{\hbar\omega/k_{\mathrm B}T}-1}\,\dd\omega$. Setting $x=\hbar\omega/k_{\mathrm B}T$, $\theta_{\mathrm D}=\hbar\omega_{\mathrm D}/k_{\mathrm B}$, and $y=\theta_{\mathrm D}/T$, we have $\omega\,\dd\omega=(k_{\mathrm B}T/\hbar)^2x\,\dd x$, so

$$ U=\frac{N\hbar}{\omega_{\mathrm D}}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm B}T}{\hbar}\right)^2\int_0^y\frac{x}{e^x-1}\dd x=Nk_{\mathrm B}T\cdot\frac{T}{\theta_{\mathrm D}}\,F_1(y),\qquad F_1(y)\equiv\int_0^y\frac{x}{e^x-1}\dd x $$

Differentiate with respect to $T$. Using $\dd y/\dd T=-y/T$ and $F_1'(y)=y/(e^y-1)$, we obtain

$$ C_V=\diff{U}{T}=Nk_{\mathrm B}\left[\frac{2T}{\theta_{\mathrm D}}F_1(y)+\frac{T^2}{\theta_{\mathrm D}}\cdot\frac{y}{e^y-1}\cdot\left(-\frac{y}{T}\right)\right]=Nk_{\mathrm B}\frac{T}{\theta_{\mathrm D}}\left[2F_1(y)-\frac{y^2}{e^y-1}\right] $$

By integration by parts, $\displaystyle\int_0^y\frac{x^2e^x}{(e^x-1)^2}\dd x=\left[-\frac{x^2}{e^x-1}\right]_0^y+2\int_0^y\frac{x}{e^x-1}\dd x=-\frac{y^2}{e^y-1}+2F_1(y)$, so the expression in the brackets is equal to this integral, giving

$$ C_V=Nk_{\mathrm B}\frac{T}{\theta_{\mathrm D}}\int_0^{\theta_{\mathrm D}/T}\frac{x^2e^x}{(e^x-1)^2}\dd x $$

as the result.

(c) At low temperatures ($T\ll\theta_{\mathrm D}$), we set the upper limit to $\infty$ and use Formula 47.5 ($p=2$), $\int_0^\infty x^2e^x/(e^x-1)^2\,\dd x=2!\,\zeta(2)=2\cdot\pi^2/6=\pi^2/3$, which gives $C_V=\dfrac{\pi^2}{3}Nk_{\mathrm B}\dfrac T{\theta_{\mathrm D}}$, proportional to the temperature (the $T^3$ of three dimensions becomes $T^1$ in one dimension). At high temperatures ($T\gg\theta_{\mathrm D}$), $y\ll1$ and the integrand $x^2e^x/(e^x-1)^2\approx1$, so the integral becomes $\int_0^y\dd x=y=\theta_{\mathrm D}/T$, and we obtain $C_V=Nk_{\mathrm B}\dfrac T{\theta_{\mathrm D}}\cdot\dfrac{\theta_{\mathrm D}}T=Nk_{\mathrm B}$. This is the Dulong–Petit value for a one-dimensional chain ($k_{\mathrm B}$ per atom, since there is one vibrational degree of freedom). Checking by numerical integration, the slope on the low-temperature side is $C_V/(Nk_{\mathrm B}T/\theta_{\mathrm D})\to3.2899=\pi^2/3$, and on the high-temperature side $C_V/Nk_{\mathrm B}\to0.99993$.

Exercise 53.9: Explain why the upper limit may be taken to be $\infty$, how the Debye model differs from the Einstein model, and how photons and phonons compare

Solution

(a) With $y=\theta_{\mathrm D}/T$, we can write $\int_0^yx^3/(e^x-1)\,\dd x=\pi^4/15-\int_y^\infty x^3/(e^x-1)\,\dd x$. The error caused by setting the upper limit to $\infty$ is the second term on the right-hand side (the remaining integral). Since the integrand becomes exponentially small for large $x$, like $x^3e^{-x}$, the remaining integral is very small. By numerical integration, it is $0.062$ at $y=10$ (about $0.96\%$ of the total value $\pi^4/15=6.494$) and $1.9\times10^{-5}$ at $y=20$ (about $3\times10^{-6}$ times the total). $T\ll\theta_{\mathrm D}$ means that $y$ is large, so the error from setting the upper limit to $\infty$ becomes small rapidly as the temperature is lowered. Physically, this means that the modes of high frequency with $\hbar\omega\gg k_{\mathrm B}T$ (near $\omega_{\mathrm D}$) are not excited by thermal energy and are frozen out, so whether or not the upper limit $\omega_{\mathrm D}$ exists does not affect the low-temperature specific heat.

(b) In the Einstein model, all $3N$ modes have a single angular frequency $\omega_{\mathrm E}$ (the density of states is concentrated at the single point $\omega_{\mathrm E}$). Since the lowest excitation energy is the finite value $\hbar\omega_{\mathrm E}$ (a gap), for $k_{\mathrm B}T\ll\hbar\omega_{\mathrm E}$ the probability of excitation becomes exponentially small, in proportion to $e^{-\hbar\omega_{\mathrm E}/k_{\mathrm B}T}$, and the specific heat also goes to $0$ exponentially. In contrast, in the density of states of the Debye model, $g\propto\omega^2$, the modes continue all the way down to $\omega\to0$. However low the temperature, there exist long-wavelength modes satisfying $\hbar\omega\lesssim k_{\mathrm B}T$; their number is $\omega^3\propto T^3$, and the energy of each mode is of the order of $k_{\mathrm B}T$, so $U\propto T^4$ and $C_V\propto T^3$. In other words, the difference comes from the difference in the assumption about the density of states: it is concentrated at a single point (Einstein), or it is continuous down to $\omega\to0$ (Debye).

(c) The result is as in the following table.

ItemPhotonsPhonons (Debye model)
SimilaritiesEach mode is a harmonic oscillator with energy $\hbar\omega(n+\tfrac12)$. They are bosons with $\mu=0$ whose number is not conserved. The mean occupation number is $1/(e^{\hbar\omega/k_{\mathrm B}T}-1)$. The calculation proceeds in three steps: “count the modes → Bose distribution → the integral of Chapter 47.”
Differences: type of waveelectromagnetic wave (transverse wave)elastic wave (one longitudinal, two transverse)
Dispersion relation$\omega=ck$$\omega=c_sk$ (speed of sound $c_s$)
Number of polarizations23
Total number of modesinfinite (no upper limit)$3N$ (cut off at $\omega_{\mathrm D}$)
When the temperature is raisedkeeps increasing, with $u\propto T^4$ and $C_V\propto T^3$saturates at $C_V\to3Nk_{\mathrm B}$ (Dulong–Petit)
Where they existexist even in vacuumexist only inside a crystal, and $\hbar k$ is the crystal momentum

(d) Chapter 49 of University Mathematics (AD 02) showed the road from the failure of the equipartition theorem to explain black-body radiation (the ultraviolet catastrophe) to Planck's radiation law via the quantum hypothesis, as material for mathematics such as the Boltzmann distribution, the counting of standing waves, and Fourier series. This chapter re-derives the same formula from the general theory of statistical mechanics, namely that “photons are bosons with $\mu=0$ whose number is not conserved,” and limits the counting of the density of states to a restatement of the key points. On that basis it treats the topics that are not in University Mathematics, namely the $T^4$ law for the total energy and the Stefan–Boltzmann constant (the integral of Chapter 47), the pressure and entropy of the photon gas, the cosmic microwave background, and phonons and the Debye model.

Exercise 53.10: The cosmic microwave background and the expansion of the universe

Solution

(a) The peak in terms of frequency is $\nu_{\max}=2.821\,k_{\mathrm B}T_0/h=2.821\times1.380649\times10^{-23}\times2.725/(6.62607\times10^{-34})=1.60\times10^{11}\ \mathrm{Hz}=160\ \mathrm{GHz}$. The peak in terms of wavelength follows from Wien's displacement law: $\lambda_{\max}=b/T_0=2.898\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{m\,K}/2.725\ \mathrm K=1.06\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm m=1.06\ \mathrm{mm}$. Note that this does not coincide with $c/\nu_{\max}=1.87\ \mathrm{mm}$ (Section 53.4 of the main text).

(b) Substituting $k_{\mathrm B}T_0/(\hbar c)=1.190\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{m^{-1}}$ into $n_\gamma=\dfrac{2\zeta(3)}{\pi^2}\left(\dfrac{k_{\mathrm B}T_0}{\hbar c}\right)^3$, we obtain $n_\gamma=0.2436\times(1.190\times10^{3})^3=4.1\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}=4.1\times10^{2}\ \mathrm{cm^{-3}}$ (about $410$ photons per $\mathrm{cm^3}$).

(c) When the length doubles, the wavelength of light is also stretched by a factor of two (redshift), and the temperature is halved because $T\propto1/s$: $T=T_0/2=1.3625\ \mathrm K$. The volume becomes $2^3=8$ times larger, and the total number of photons does not change (photons are neither created nor annihilated), so the photon number density becomes $1/8$: $n_\gamma\to51\ \mathrm{cm^{-3}}$. This also agrees with $n_\gamma\propto T^3$ ($(1/2)^3=1/8$). From $u=aT^4$, the energy density becomes $(1/2)^4=1/16$: $u=4.17\times10^{-14}/16=2.6\times10^{-15}\ \mathrm{J/m^3}$. This is also consistent with the fact that the number of photons becomes $1/8$ and the energy $h\nu$ of each photon becomes $1/2$, so that $u$ becomes $1/8\times1/2=1/16$.

Exercise 53.11: The dispersion relation of an atomic chain and the difference from the Debye approximation

Solution

$\sqrt{\kappa/m}=\sqrt{40/(1.0\times10^{-25})}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}=2.0\times10^{13}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, and the speed of sound is $c_s=a\sqrt{\kappa/m}=5.0\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{m/s}$ (Example 53.10).

(a) Since $ka=\pi/2$, $\sin(ka/2)=\sin(\pi/4)=1/\sqrt2$. The actual angular frequency is $\omega=2\sqrt{\kappa/m}\times\dfrac{1}{\sqrt2}=\sqrt2\times2.0\times10^{13}=2.83\times10^{13}\ \mathrm{rad/s}$.

(b) In the Debye approximation, $\omega=c_sk=a\sqrt{\kappa/m}\cdot\dfrac{\pi}{2a}=\dfrac\pi2\sqrt{\kappa/m}=1.5708\times2.0\times10^{13}=3.14\times10^{13}\ \mathrm{rad/s}$. The difference from the actual value is $3.14\times10^{13}-2.83\times10^{13}=3.1\times10^{12}\ \mathrm{rad/s}$, and as a fraction it is $\dfrac{\pi/2}{\sqrt2}-1=0.111$; that is, the Debye approximation overestimates by about $11\%$.

(c) The wavelength is $\lambda=2\pi/k=2\pi\cdot2a/\pi=4a=1.0\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm m=1.0\ \mathrm{nm}$ (four times the atomic spacing).

(d) At $k=\pi/a$ (wavelength $2a$) in Example 53.10(3), the Debye value $\pi\sqrt{\kappa/m}$ was about $57\%$ larger than the actual maximum $2\sqrt{\kappa/m}$. At $k=\pi/(2a)$ the difference is about $11\%$, and the smaller $k$ is (the longer the wavelength), the smaller the difference becomes. Indeed, for $ka\ll1$, $2\sin(ka/2)\approx ka$ and the difference approaches $0$ (about $1\%$ at $ka=0.5$). The Debye approximation is accurate for long-wavelength modes, which corresponds to the fact that it is the long-wavelength modes that determine the thermal properties at low temperatures.

← Back to Chapter 53, “Statistics of Photons and Phonons — Black-Body Radiation and the Debye Model”

Chapter 54 From Galilean Relativity to the Lorentz Transformation

Exercise 54.1: Resistive force and the Galilean transformation

Solution

In frame S (the coordinate system in which the air is at rest), the equation of motion is $m\ddot{\bm{r}}=-k\dot{\bm{r}}$. Since S′ moves with a constant velocity $\bm{v}$ relative to S, we use the Galilean transformation $\bm{r}'=\bm{r}-\bm{v}t$, that is, $\bm{r}=\bm{r}'+\bm{v}t$. Since $\bm{v}$ is a constant vector, differentiating both sides with respect to $t$ gives

$$ \dot{\bm{r}}=\dot{\bm{r}}'+\bm{v},\qquad\ddot{\bm{r}}=\ddot{\bm{r}}' $$

Substituting this into the equation of motion, the equation in frame S′ is

$$ m\ddot{\bm{r}}'=-k\left(\dot{\bm{r}}'+\bm{v}\right)=-k\dot{\bm{r}}'-k\bm{v} $$

The right-hand side has acquired a constant term $-k\bm{v}$ that was absent in frame S, so the equation does not take exactly the same form as in frame S, namely $m\ddot{\bm{r}}'=-k\dot{\bm{r}}'$. The only exception is $\bm{v}=\bm{0}$ (when S′ is at rest relative to the air).

However, this does not mean that “Galilean relativity is violated.” Seen from frame S′, the air moves with velocity $\bm{u}'_{\text{air}}=\bm{0}-\bm{v}=-\bm{v}$. If we therefore use the particle's “velocity relative to the air,” $\dot{\bm{r}}'-\bm{u}'_{\text{air}}=\dot{\bm{r}}'+\bm{v}$, the equation in frame S′ becomes

$$ m\ddot{\bm{r}}'=-k\left(\dot{\bm{r}}'-\bm{u}'_{\text{air}}\right) $$

This has the same form as the equation in frame S, $m\ddot{\bm{r}}=-k(\dot{\bm{r}}-\bm{u}_{\text{air}})$ ($\bm{u}_{\text{air}}=\bm{0}$). In other words, if the law of the resistive force is written not in terms of “the velocity of the particle” but in terms of “the velocity of the particle relative to the medium (air),” and the air is transformed along with it as part of the physical system, then the form of the equation of motion is unchanged by the Galilean transformation. Even when the force depends on velocity, Galilean relativity holds as long as what matters is the relative velocity. (“$\bm{F}=-k\dot{\bm{r}}$” was an equation of a form that holds only in the coordinate system in which the air is at rest.)

Exercise 54.2: Values of the Lorentz factor

Solution

(a) Since $\beta=0.8$, $1-\beta^2=1-0.64=0.36$ and $\sqrt{0.36}=0.6$, so

$$ \gamma=\frac{1}{0.6}=\frac53\approx1.667 $$

(b) Since $\beta=0.99$, $1-\beta^2=1-0.9801=0.0199$ and $\sqrt{0.0199}=0.14107$, so

$$ \gamma=\frac{1}{0.14107}\approx7.089 $$

(c) Squaring both sides of $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-\beta^2}$ and taking reciprocals gives $1-\beta^2=1/\gamma^2$, that is, $\beta^2=1-1/\gamma^2$. For $\gamma=10$, $\beta^2=1-1/100=0.99$ and $\beta=\sqrt{0.99}=0.99499$. That is, $v=0.995c\approx2.98\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s}$. (d) For $\gamma=2$, $\beta^2=1-1/4=3/4$ and $\beta=\sqrt3/2\approx0.866$ ($v\approx0.866c$).

Exercise 54.3: Fringe shift of a Michelson interferometer

Solution

$\beta=v/c=3.0\times10^4/(3.00\times10^8)=1.0\times10^{-4}$ and $\beta^2=1.0\times10^{-8}$.

(a) The difference in round-trip times is $\Delta t\approx(L/c)\beta^2$. Here

$$ \frac{L}{c}=\frac{2.0\ \mathrm m}{3.00\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s}}=6.67\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm s $$

and multiplying by $\beta^2$ (dimensionless) gives

$$ \Delta t=\frac{L}{c}\,\beta^2=6.67\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm s\times1.0\times10^{-8}=6.7\times10^{-17}\ \mathrm s $$

(b) The optical path difference is $c\Delta t=3.00\times10^8\times6.67\times10^{-17}=2.0\times10^{-8}\ \mathrm m$. When the arms are interchanged (rotated by $90^\circ$), the sign of $\Delta t$ reverses, so the number of fringes shifted is $2\times c\Delta t/\lambda$:

$$ 2\times\frac{2.0\times10^{-8}\ \mathrm m}{6.0\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm m}=0.067 $$

That is, about $7\ \%$ of the fringe spacing, a small shift that is not clearly visible to the naked eye. (c) The value of $L$ for which the fringe shift satisfies $2L\beta^2/\lambda=0.4$ is

$$ L=\frac{0.4\,\lambda}{2\beta^2}=\frac{0.4\times6.0\times10^{-7}\ \mathrm m}{2\times1.0\times10^{-8}}=12\ \mathrm m $$

Since making the arm itself $12\ \mathrm m$ long is unrealistic, Michelson and Morley made the light travel back and forth many times with mirrors, bringing the effective arm length to about $11\ \mathrm m$.

Exercise 54.4: Determining $A,B,C,D$ from conditions on light rays

Solution

For the linear transformation $x'=Ax+B\,ct$, $ct'=Cx+D\,ct$, we use the four conditions in turn.

(i) For light with $x=ct$, $x'=(A+B)\,ct$ and $ct'=(C+D)\,ct$, so the condition for $x'=ct'$ is

$$ A+B=C+D \qquad\cdots\ \text{①} $$

(ii) For light with $x=-ct$, $x'=(-A+B)\,ct$ and $ct'=(-C+D)\,ct$, and the condition for $x'=-ct'$ is $-A+B=-(-C+D)=C-D$, that is,

$$ -A+B=C-D \qquad\cdots\ \text{②} $$

From ①$+$② we get $2B=2C$, that is, $B=C$. From ①$-$② we get $2A=2D$, that is, $A=D$.

(iii) The origin $x'=0$ of S′ corresponds to $x=vt$ in frame S, so $0=Avt+B\,ct$, that is, $B=-\beta A$ ($\beta=v/c$). Therefore $C=B=-\beta A$ and $D=A$, and the transformation has been narrowed down to the form

$$ x'=A\,(x-vt),\qquad ct'=A\,(ct-\beta x) $$

Only the value of $A$ remains.

(iv) By the principle of relativity, the inverse transformation is obtained by the replacement $\beta\to-\beta$ ($v\to-v$), so $x=A\,(x'+\beta\,ct')$. Substituting the above $x',ct'$ here gives

$$ x=A\left[A\,(x-\beta ct)+\beta A\,(ct-\beta x)\right]=A^2\left[x-\beta ct+\beta ct-\beta^2x\right]=A^2(1-\beta^2)\,x $$

Since this holds for arbitrary $x$, $A^2(1-\beta^2)=1$, that is, $A^2=1/(1-\beta^2)=\gamma^2$. We take $A=+\gamma$ so that $x'\to x$ as $v\to0$ ($A\to1$). In the end,

$$ A=D=\gamma,\qquad B=C=-\beta\gamma $$

which agrees with the result in the main text of Chapter 54 (the one obtained by comparing the coefficients of $x^2-c^2t^2$). In this method, without using the invariance of $x^2-c^2t^2$ from the start, we have derived the Lorentz transformation from the invariance of the speed of light (the light-ray conditions (i) and (ii)) and the principle of relativity ((iv)) alone.

Exercise 54.5: Correspondence between the Galilean and Lorentz transformations

Solution

Placing the Galilean transformation and the Lorentz transformation ($\beta=v/c$, $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-\beta^2}$) side by side gives the following.

  • (a) $x'$: Galilean transformation $x'=x-vt$. Lorentz transformation $x'=\gamma\,(x-vt)$.
  • (b) $y',z'$: in both, $y'=y$ and $z'=z$.
  • (c) $t'$: Galilean transformation $t'=t$. Lorentz transformation $t'=\gamma\left(t-\dfrac{v}{c^2}x\right)$.
  • (d) Inverse transformation for $x$: Galilean transformation $x=x'+vt'$. Lorentz transformation $x=\gamma\,(x'+vt')$.
  • (e) Inverse transformation for $t$: Galilean transformation $t=t'$. Lorentz transformation $t=\gamma\left(t'+\dfrac{v}{c^2}x'\right)$.
  • (f) The speed, in frame S′, of light traveling in the $+x$ direction with speed $c$ in frame S ($x=ct$): with the Galilean transformation, $x'=x-vt=(c-v)t$ and $t'=t$, so $x'/t'=c-v$. With the Lorentz transformation, as in Example 54.9 of Chapter 54, $x'=ct'$, so the speed is $c$.

We examine the limit $\beta\to0$ ($v\ll c$, and the speed of the object $u\ll c$). Since $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-\beta^2}\to1$, the Lorentz transformations in (a) and (d) become $x'\to x-vt$ and $x\to x'+vt'$, which coincide with the Galilean transformation. The correction terms in (c) and (e) are $vx/c^2$ and $vx'/c^2$, and their ratio to $t$ is $\beta\,u/c\ll1$, so they can be neglected (Section 54.6), and $t'\to t$ and $t\to t'$, which coincide with the Galilean transformation. In (f) the speeds are $c-v$ and $c$, and their relative difference is $v/c\to0$. Therefore, for every quantity, the Lorentz transformation coincides with the Galilean transformation as $v/c\to0$.

Exercise 54.6: The Lorentz transformation in an arbitrary direction

Solution

Let $r_n=\bm{r}\cdot\bm{n}$ (the component of $\bm{r}$ in the direction of $\bm{n}$). If we regard the direction of $\bm{n}$ as a new “$x$ axis,” the coordinate $r_n$ parallel to $\bm{n}$ undergoes the same transformation as $x$ in Theorem 54.1 of Chapter 54, and the perpendicular component $\bm{r}_\perp=\bm{r}-r_n\bm{n}$ is unchanged, just like $y,z$, as follows:

$$ r'_n=\gamma\,(r_n-vt),\qquad\bm{r}'_\perp=\bm{r}_\perp,\qquad t'=\gamma\left(t-\frac{v}{c^2}r_n\right) $$

Substituting the above into $\bm{r}'=r'_n\bm{n}+\bm{r}'_\perp$ gives

$$ \bm{r}'=\gamma\,(r_n-vt)\,\bm{n}+\bm{r}-r_n\bm{n}=\bm{r}+(\gamma-1)(\bm{r}\cdot\bm{n})\,\bm{n}-\gamma vt\,\bm{n} $$

For the time, using $v\,r_n=\bm{v}\cdot\bm{r}$ ($\bm{v}=v\bm{n}$), we have $t'=\gamma\left(t-\bm{v}\cdot\bm{r}/c^2\right)$.

Invariant: Since $\bm{r}'^2=(r'_n)^2+\bm{r}'^2_\perp=\gamma^2(r_n-vt)^2+(\bm{r}^2-r_n^2)$ and $c^2t'^2=\gamma^2(ct-\beta r_n)^2$,

$$ c^2t'^2-\bm{r}'^2=\gamma^2\left[(ct-\beta r_n)^2-(r_n-\beta ct)^2\right]-\bm{r}^2+r_n^2 $$

The expression in the brackets is $c^2t^2-2\beta ct\,r_n+\beta^2r_n^2-r_n^2+2\beta ct\,r_n-\beta^2c^2t^2=(1-\beta^2)(c^2t^2-r_n^2)$, so using $\gamma^2(1-\beta^2)=1$ we obtain

$$ c^2t'^2-\bm{r}'^2=(c^2t^2-r_n^2)-\bm{r}^2+r_n^2=c^2t^2-\bm{r}^2 $$

Hence the invariant is preserved.

The case $\bm{n}=\bm{e}_x$: Here $r_n=x$ and $\bm{r}_\perp=(0,y,z)$, so $x'=\gamma(x-vt)$, $y'=y$, $z'=z$, and $t'=\gamma(t-vx/c^2)$, which agrees with Theorem 54.1 of Chapter 54. Note that the assumption $y=y'$, $z=z'$ concerns the components perpendicular to the direction of motion ($\bm{n}$); for motion in a general direction, the same argument holds if it is read as “the components perpendicular to $\bm{n}$ are unchanged.”

Exercise 54.7: Are frames with $v\ge c$ allowed?

Solution

(a) For $\beta=1.2$, $1-\beta^2=1-1.44=-0.44\lt0$. We have $\sqrt{-0.44}=i\sqrt{0.44}=0.663\,i$ ($i$ is the imaginary unit), and $\gamma=1/(0.663\,i)=-1.51\,i$ is not real. Therefore the $x',t'$ of the Lorentz transformation are not real either, and have no meaning as coordinates and times that can actually be measured. That is, the relative velocity of two inertial frames connected by a Lorentz transformation must satisfy $\abs{v}\lt c$.

(b) As in Table 54.1 of Chapter 54, $\gamma$ is $2.3$ at $\beta=0.9$, $7.1$ at $\beta=0.99$, and $22.4$ at $\beta=0.999$; it grows without bound as $\beta\to1$. As will be shown in Chapter 57, the energy of an object of mass $m$ is $E=\gamma mc^2$, that is, it is $\gamma$ times the rest energy $mc^2$ (the momentum also grows, in the form $\gamma mv$). To bring an object close to the speed of light, the energy and momentum of the object grow rapidly for even a slight increase in speed, so the energy that must be supplied keeps increasing, and an infinite amount of energy is required to reach $v=c$. In other words, the mathematical fact that $\gamma\to\infty$ corresponds to the fact that the speed of an object with mass cannot exceed $c$ (cannot even reach it).

Exercise 54.8: Composition of two Lorentz transformations

Solution

(a) For $\beta=0.6$, $\gamma=5/4$ and $\beta\gamma=3/4$, so $\Lambda(0.6)=\begin{pmatrix}5/4&-3/4\\-3/4&5/4\end{pmatrix}$. The product is

$$ \Lambda(0.6)\Lambda(0.6)=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{25}{16}+\frac{9}{16}&-\frac{15}{16}-\frac{15}{16}\\-\frac{15}{16}-\frac{15}{16}&\frac{9}{16}+\frac{25}{16}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}17/8&-15/8\\-15/8&17/8\end{pmatrix} $$

(b) Comparing this with $\begin{pmatrix}\gamma&-\beta\gamma\\-\beta\gamma&\gamma\end{pmatrix}$, we find $\gamma=17/8=2.125$ and $\beta\gamma=15/8$. Therefore $\beta=(15/8)/(17/8)=15/17\approx0.882$. Verification: $\gamma^2-(\beta\gamma)^2=(289-225)/64=1$, and $1/\sqrt{1-(15/17)^2}=1/\sqrt{64/289}=17/8$, which agrees with $\gamma$.

(c) For general $\beta_1,\beta_2$ ($\gamma_1,\gamma_2$), we have

$$ \Lambda(\beta_1)\Lambda(\beta_2)=\gamma_1\gamma_2\begin{pmatrix}1&-\beta_1\\-\beta_1&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\beta_2\\-\beta_2&1\end{pmatrix}=\gamma_1\gamma_2\begin{pmatrix}1+\beta_1\beta_2&-(\beta_1+\beta_2)\\-(\beta_1+\beta_2)&1+\beta_1\beta_2\end{pmatrix} $$

Setting this equal to $\Lambda(\beta)$, the $(1,1)$ entry gives $\gamma=\gamma_1\gamma_2(1+\beta_1\beta_2)$ and the $(1,2)$ entry gives $\beta\gamma=\gamma_1\gamma_2(\beta_1+\beta_2)$, so taking the ratio yields

$$ \beta=\frac{\beta_1+\beta_2}{1+\beta_1\beta_2} $$

(The factor $\gamma_1\gamma_2>0$ cancels. Also, since $1-\beta^2=\dfrac{(1+\beta_1\beta_2)^2-(\beta_1+\beta_2)^2}{(1+\beta_1\beta_2)^2}=\dfrac{(1-\beta_1^2)(1-\beta_2^2)}{(1+\beta_1\beta_2)^2}$, we get $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-\beta^2}=\gamma_1\gamma_2(1+\beta_1\beta_2)$, which is also consistent with the $(1,1)$ entry.)

(d) In the Galilean composition of velocities, $0.6c+0.6c=1.2c$ exceeds the speed of light, but in the composition of Lorentz transformations, $\beta=(0.6+0.6)/(1+0.36)=1.2/1.36=15/17\approx0.882$, which does not exceed the speed of light. In general, if $\abs{\beta_1},\abs{\beta_2}\lt1$, then $1-\beta^2=(1-\beta_1^2)(1-\beta_2^2)/(1+\beta_1\beta_2)^2\gt0$, so $\abs{\beta}\lt1$. Also, when $\beta_1,\beta_2\ll1$, the denominator is close to $1$ and $\beta\approx\beta_1+\beta_2$, which agrees with the composition of velocities in high school physics. This formula appears again in Chapter 55 as the velocity addition law (the Lorentz velocity transformation).

← Back to Chapter 54, “From Galilean Relativity to the Lorentz Transformation”

Chapter 55 Lorentz Contraction, Time Dilation, and the Transformation of Velocities

Exercise 55.1: Values of the Lorentz contraction

Solution

(a) When $\beta=0.5$, $1-\beta^2=1-0.25=0.75$, so

$$ \frac{\ell'}{\ell}=\sqrt{0.75}=\frac{\sqrt3}{2}=0.866 $$

Thus the length of the rod shrinks to about $86.6\%$. (b) When $\beta=0.9$, $1-\beta^2=1-0.81=0.19$, so

$$ \frac{\ell'}{\ell}=\sqrt{0.19}=0.4359 $$

and the rod shrinks to about $43.6\%$. (c) Setting $\ell'/\ell=1/2$ gives $\sqrt{1-\beta^2}=1/2$. Squaring both sides gives $1-\beta^2=1/4$, that is, $\beta^2=3/4$, so the required speed is

$$ \beta=\frac{\sqrt3}{2}=0.866,\qquad v=0.866\,c $$

(d) Setting $\ell'/\ell=1\ \mathrm{mm}/1\ \mathrm m=10^{-3}$ gives $\sqrt{1-\beta^2}=10^{-3}$, so $1-\beta^2=10^{-6}$. When $\beta$ is very close to 1, we can approximate $1-\beta^2=(1-\beta)(1+\beta)\approx2\,(1-\beta)$ (since $1+\beta\approx2$), so

$$ 1-\beta\approx\frac{10^{-6}}{2}=5\times10^{-7} $$

that is, this happens when $\beta=0.9999995$ ($v=0.9999995\,c$). In this case $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-\beta^2}=10^{3}$ (and $\ell'/\ell=1/\gamma$).

∎

Exercise 55.2: The youthfulness of a space traveler

Solution

From $\beta=0.8$ we have $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-0.64}=1/0.6=5/3$ and $1/\gamma=0.6$. (a) In Earth's frame, the spacecraft travels a distance of $4$ light-years at speed $0.8c$, so the time taken is

$$ \Delta t=\frac{4\ \text{light-years}}{0.8\,c}=\frac{4}{0.8}\ \text{years}=5\ \text{years} $$

(b) The clock on the spacecraft measures the interval between departure and arrival, which occur at the same place inside the spacecraft, so it reads the proper time $\Delta\tau$. By time dilation ($\Delta t=\gamma\Delta\tau$),

$$ \Delta\tau=\frac{\Delta t}{\gamma}=5\ \text{years}\times0.6=3\ \text{years} $$

(c) In the spacecraft's frame, the Earth (the point of departure) and the destination star move toward the spacecraft (backward) at speed $0.8c$. The distance between the two bodies is the proper length of $4$ light-years in the frame in which the two bodies are at rest (Earth's frame); in the spacecraft's frame it is Lorentz-contracted to

$$ \ell'=\frac{4\ \text{light-years}}{\gamma}=4\times0.6=2.4\ \text{light-years} $$

Since the star approaches at speed $0.8c$ and covers this distance, the time taken is

$$ \Delta\tau=\frac{2.4\ \text{light-years}}{0.8\,c}=3\ \text{years} $$

which agrees with the answer to (b). Time dilation (the explanation in Earth's frame) and length contraction (the explanation in the spacecraft's frame) explain the same result in two ways. (A round trip at the same speed takes $6$ years on the spacecraft's clock and $10$ years on Earth. The treatment of a round trip that includes accelerated motion is examined in Chapter 56.)

∎

Exercise 55.3: The low-speed limit of the Doppler effect

Solution

(a) We take the Taylor expansion of $f(\beta)=(1-\beta)^{1/2}(1+\beta)^{-1/2}$ about $\beta=0$, factor by factor. For $(1-\beta)^{1/2}$: since $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd\beta}(1-\beta)^{1/2}=-\dfrac12(1-\beta)^{-1/2}$ equals $-\dfrac12$ at $\beta=0$, we obtain $1-\dfrac{\beta}{2}+\cdots$. For $(1+\beta)^{-1/2}$: since $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd\beta}(1+\beta)^{-1/2}=-\dfrac12(1+\beta)^{-3/2}$ equals $-\dfrac12$ at $\beta=0$, we obtain $1-\dfrac{\beta}{2}+\cdots$. Therefore, to first order in $\beta$,

$$ \sqrt{\frac{1-\beta}{1+\beta}}\approx\left(1-\frac\beta2\right)\left(1-\frac\beta2\right)\approx1-\beta $$

which shows that $\nu'\approx\nu(1-\beta)$ (terms of order $\beta^2$ and higher were neglected). This agrees with the classical Doppler effect (for an observer receding at speed $v$, the frequency is $\nu(1-v/c)$).

(b) For $\beta=0.01$, $(1-\beta)/(1+\beta)=0.99/1.01=0.980198$, and its square root is $0.990050$ (to five significant figures). The approximate value is $1-\beta=0.99$, so the difference is

$$ 0.990050-0.99=5.0\times10^{-5} $$

(more precisely, $4.95\times10^{-5}$). (c) We expand to second order. Since $(1-\beta)^{1/2}=1-\dfrac\beta2-\dfrac{\beta^2}{8}+\cdots$ and $(1+\beta)^{-1/2}=1-\dfrac\beta2+\dfrac{3\beta^2}{8}+\cdots$ (by the binomial expansion $(1+x)^{\alpha}=1+\alpha x+\dfrac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{2}x^2+\cdots$), the coefficient of $\beta^2$ in the product is $1\cdot\dfrac38+\left(-\dfrac12\right)\left(-\dfrac12\right)+\left(-\dfrac18\right)\cdot1=\dfrac38+\dfrac14-\dfrac18=\dfrac12$. Hence

$$ \sqrt{\frac{1-\beta}{1+\beta}}=1-\beta+\frac{\beta^2}{2}-\cdots $$

and the difference between the exact value and the approximation $1-\beta$ is approximately $\beta^2/2=(0.01)^2/2=5\times10^{-5}$, which agrees with the value in (b) (including the next term, $-\beta^3/2=-5\times10^{-7}$, gives $4.95\times10^{-5}$). The relativistic correction is a small quantity of order $\beta^2/2$.

∎

Exercise 55.4: The rocket problem ($v=0.8c$)

Solution

Since $\beta=0.8$, $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-0.64}=5/3$. Let the reading of the rocket's clock be $\tau=1$ hour $=60$ minutes. (a) In Earth's frame, the moving rocket's clock runs at the rate $1/\gamma$, so the rocket's clock reads $\tau$ when the Earth clock reads

$$ t_{\mathrm E}=\gamma\tau=\frac53\times60\ \text{minutes}=100\ \text{minutes}=1\ \text{hour}\,40\ \text{minutes} $$

(b) What we see through the telescope is the moment at which the light that left the rocket at this time (Earth time $t_{\mathrm E}$) reaches Earth. The rocket is at the position $x_{\mathrm E}=vt_{\mathrm E}$, and the light takes a time $x_{\mathrm E}/c=\beta t_{\mathrm E}$ to reach Earth, so the time at which it is seen is

$$ t=t_{\mathrm E}+\beta t_{\mathrm E}=(1+\beta)\,\gamma\tau=\sqrt{\frac{1+\beta}{1-\beta}}\ \tau=\sqrt{\frac{1.8}{0.2}}\times60\ \text{minutes}=3\times60\ \text{minutes}=180\ \text{minutes} $$

that is, $3$ hours after departure (3 hours 00 minutes) (this can also be checked from $(1+\beta)\gamma=1.8\times5/3=3$). (c) The difference between (b) and (a) is $180-100=80$ minutes. On the other hand, the position of the rocket is $x_{\mathrm E}=vt_{\mathrm E}=0.8c\times100\ \text{minutes}=80\ \text{light-minutes}$ (the distance light travels in 80 minutes), so the time for the light to travel from the rocket to Earth is $x_{\mathrm E}/c=80\ \text{minutes}$. These two agree.

∎

Exercise 55.5: Composing velocities in two steps

Solution

(a) The velocity of S′ relative to S is $v=\beta_1c$, and the velocity of the object in the S′ frame is $u'_x=\beta_2c$. By the first equation of the inverse velocity transformation, the velocity of the object in the S frame is

$$ u_x=\frac{u'_x+v}{1+\dfrac{vu'_x}{c^2}}=\frac{\beta_2c+\beta_1c}{1+\beta_1\beta_2} $$

Dividing both sides by $c$ and writing $\beta=u_x/c$, we obtain

$$ \beta=\frac{\beta_1+\beta_2}{1+\beta_1\beta_2} $$

(b) We compute $1-\beta^2$:

$$ 1-\beta^2=\frac{(1+\beta_1\beta_2)^2-(\beta_1+\beta_2)^2}{(1+\beta_1\beta_2)^2} $$

Expanding the numerator,

$$ \begin{aligned} (1+\beta_1\beta_2)^2-(\beta_1+\beta_2)^2&=1+2\beta_1\beta_2+\beta_1^2\beta_2^2-\beta_1^2-2\beta_1\beta_2-\beta_2^2\\ &=1-\beta_1^2-\beta_2^2+\beta_1^2\beta_2^2=(1-\beta_1^2)(1-\beta_2^2) \end{aligned} $$

(the last equality is the expansion of $(1-\beta_1^2)(1-\beta_2^2)$). Hence

$$ 1-\beta^2=\frac{(1-\beta_1^2)(1-\beta_2^2)}{(1+\beta_1\beta_2)^2} $$

If $0\le\beta_1,\beta_2<1$, then $1-\beta_1^2>0$ and $1-\beta_2^2>0$, so the right-hand side is positive; hence $1-\beta^2>0$, that is, $\beta<1$ (that $\beta\ge0$ is clear from the formula in (a)). No matter how close $\beta_1,\beta_2$ are brought to 1, the composite speed never exceeds the speed of light. (c) For $\beta_1=\beta_2=0.9$,

$$ \beta=\frac{0.9+0.9}{1+0.9\times0.9}=\frac{1.8}{1.81}=\frac{180}{181}=0.9945 $$

The simple sum $1.8$ would exceed the speed of light, but the correct value is $0.9945$, which is 99.45 percent of $c$.

∎

Exercise 55.6: A muon with speed $0.98c$

Solution

(a) Since $\beta=0.98$, $1-\beta^2=1-0.9604=0.0396$ and $\sqrt{0.0396}=0.19900$, so

$$ \gamma=\frac{1}{0.19900}=5.025 $$

The mean lifetime as seen from the ground is $\gamma T_\mu=5.025\times2.197\ \mathrm{\mu s}=11.04\ \mathrm{\mu s}$. (b) The mean flight distance as seen from the ground, with $c=2.998\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{m/s}$, is

$$ L=v\,(\gamma T_\mu)=0.98\times2.998\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{m/s}\times11.04\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm s=3.24\times10^{3}\ \mathrm m $$

that is, about $3.2\ \mathrm{km}$. (c) The fraction of muons descending from an altitude $h=12\ \mathrm{km}$ that arrive without decaying is, because the proper time $h/(\gamma v)$ elapses during the descent time $h/v$ in the ground frame,

$$ P=\exp\left(-\frac{h/(\gamma v)}{T_\mu}\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{h}{L}\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{12\,000}{3\,244}\right)=\exp(-3.70)=0.0247 $$

which is about $2.5\%$. (d) In the muon's frame, the thickness of the atmosphere is Lorentz-contracted to $12\,000\ \mathrm m/\gamma=12\,000/5.025=2\,388\ \mathrm m$. Since the ground approaches at speed $v=0.98c$, the time taken to descend (the proper time) is

$$ \tau=\frac{2\,388\ \mathrm m}{0.98\times2.998\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{m/s}}=8.13\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm s=8.13\ \mathrm{\mu s} $$

The surviving fraction is $\exp(-\tau/T_\mu)=\exp(-8.13/2.197)=\exp(-3.70)=0.0247$, which agrees with (c).

∎

Exercise 55.7: The clock on the International Space Station

Solution

(a) The speed in a circular orbit is $v=\sqrt{GM/r}$ (universal gravitation provides the centripetal force), so

$$ v=\sqrt{\frac{3.986\times10^{14}}{6.778\times10^{6}}}\ \mathrm{m/s}=\sqrt{5.881\times10^{7}}\ \mathrm{m/s}=7.67\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{m/s} $$

(b) The fractional delay due to special relativity is $v^2/(2c^2)$, and with $c^2=8.988\times10^{16}\ \mathrm{m^2/s^2}$,

$$ \frac{v^2}{2c^2}=\frac{5.881\times10^{7}}{2\times8.988\times10^{16}}=3.27\times10^{-10} $$

The delay over one day, $86\,400\ \mathrm s$, is $3.27\times10^{-10}\times86\,400\ \mathrm s=2.83\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm s=28.3\ \mathrm{\mu s}$. (c) The fractional gain due to general relativity is $\dfrac{GM}{c^2}\left(\dfrac1R-\dfrac1r\right)$, and with $GM/c^2=4.435\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm m$ and $1/R-1/r=1.5679\times10^{-7}-1.4754\times10^{-7}=9.25\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm{m^{-1}}$,

$$ \frac{GM}{c^2}\left(\frac1R-\frac1r\right)=4.435\times10^{-3}\times9.25\times10^{-9}=4.10\times10^{-11} $$

The gain over one day is $4.10\times10^{-11}\times86\,400\ \mathrm s=3.5\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm s=3.5\ \mathrm{\mu s}$. (d) The net result is

$$ -28.3\ \mathrm{\mu s}+3.5\ \mathrm{\mu s}=-24.7\ \mathrm{\mu s} $$

that is, the clock on the space station falls behind the clock on the ground by about $25\ \mathrm{\mu s}$ per day. In contrast to a GPS satellite (whose altitude is high, so that the gain of $45.6\ \mathrm{\mu s}$ is larger than the delay of $7.2\ \mathrm{\mu s}$), for the low-altitude space station the delay due to motion (special relativity) is the stronger effect. The two effects cancel when $\dfrac{GM}{2c^2r}=\dfrac{GM}{c^2}\left(\dfrac1R-\dfrac1r\right)$, that is, at $r=\dfrac32R\approx9.6\times10^{6}\ \mathrm m$ (an altitude of about $3\,200\ \mathrm{km}$); in orbits lower than this the clock falls behind, and in higher orbits it gains.

∎

Exercise 55.8: Apparent superluminal motion (the difference in light-travel times)

Solution

(a) Let $D$ be the distance from the point where the first signal was emitted to the observer. The second signal is emitted, after a time $\Delta t$, at the point to which the blob has advanced by $\beta c\,\Delta t$. Since the observer is sufficiently far away, the light rays can be regarded as parallel to the line of sight, and that point is closer to the observer by $\beta c\,\Delta t\cos\theta$. Therefore the times at which the signals reach the observer are

$$ t_1=\frac{D}{c},\qquad t_2=\Delta t+\frac{D-\beta c\,\Delta t\cos\theta}{c} $$

and their difference is

$$ \Delta t_{\mathrm{obs}}=t_2-t_1=\Delta t-\beta\,\Delta t\cos\theta=\Delta t\,(1-\beta\cos\theta) $$

If $\theta=0$, then $\Delta t_{\mathrm{obs}}=(1-\beta)\Delta t$, and the interval between the signals reaching the observer appears shortened. (b) The displacement perpendicular to the line of sight is $\beta c\,\Delta t\sin\theta$, so the apparent speed is

$$ \beta_{\mathrm{app}}c=\frac{\beta c\,\Delta t\sin\theta}{\Delta t\,(1-\beta\cos\theta)}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\beta_{\mathrm{app}}=\frac{\beta\sin\theta}{1-\beta\cos\theta} $$

(c) We substitute $\beta=0.98$ and $\theta=10^\circ$. Since $\sin10^\circ=0.173648$ and $\cos10^\circ=0.984808$,

$$ \beta_{\mathrm{app}}=\frac{0.98\times0.173648}{1-0.98\times0.984808}=\frac{0.170175}{0.034888}=4.88 $$

Although the actual speed is $0.98c$, the apparent speed is about $4.9c$. (d) Differentiating $\beta_{\mathrm{app}}(\theta)$ with the quotient rule, we obtain

$$ \frac{\dd\beta_{\mathrm{app}}}{\dd\theta}=\frac{\beta\cos\theta\,(1-\beta\cos\theta)-\beta\sin\theta\cdot\beta\sin\theta}{(1-\beta\cos\theta)^2}=\frac{\beta\cos\theta-\beta^2}{(1-\beta\cos\theta)^2}=\frac{\beta\,(\cos\theta-\beta)}{(1-\beta\cos\theta)^2} $$

(we used $\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta=1$ in the numerator). This vanishes at $\cos\theta=\beta$, is positive for $\cos\theta\gt\beta$ (the side of smaller $\theta$), and is negative for $\cos\theta\lt\beta$, so the apparent speed is maximal when $\cos\theta=\beta$. In this case $\sin\theta=\sqrt{1-\beta^2}=1/\gamma$ and $1-\beta\cos\theta=1-\beta^2=1/\gamma^2$, so

$$ \beta_{\mathrm{app,max}}=\frac{\beta\,(1/\gamma)}{1/\gamma^2}=\gamma\beta $$

(for $\beta=0.98$, $\gamma\beta=4.92$, and the corresponding angle is $\theta=\arccos0.98=11.5^\circ$). We have $\gamma\beta\gt1$ when $\beta^2\gt1-\beta^2$, that is, when $\beta\gt1/\sqrt2\approx0.707$ (for $\beta=0.9$, $\gamma\beta=2.06$).

This does not contradict relativity. The speed of the blob itself remains $\beta c\lt c$; the apparent speed exceeds $c$ because it is an apparent effect of the difference in light travel times: “since the blob emits the signals while approaching the observer, the difference $\Delta t_{\mathrm{obs}}=\Delta t\,(1-\beta\cos\theta)$ between the arrival times of the two signals is much shorter than the actual time difference $\Delta t$.” Nothing has actually been carried faster than light.

∎

Exercise 55.9: A fast-moving light clock

Solution

(a) The period of a light clock at rest is the time for light to make one round trip between mirrors separated by a distance $d$, so

$$ \Delta\tau=\frac{2d}{c}=\frac{2\times0.30\ \mathrm m}{3.00\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{m/s}}=2.00\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm s=2.00\ \mathrm{ns} $$

(b) For $\beta=0.8$, $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-0.64}=1/0.6=5/3$, so the period of the clock that appears to be moving is

$$ T=\gamma\,\Delta\tau=\frac53\times2.00\ \mathrm{ns}=3.33\ \mathrm{ns} $$

(c) The distance traveled by the light during one round trip is $cT=3.00\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{m/s}\times3.33\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm s=1.00\ \mathrm m$, which is $5/3$ times ($=\gamma$ times) the round-trip distance $2d=0.60\ \mathrm m$ when the clock is at rest. Let us check the Pythagorean theorem. The hypotenuse of a one-way trip is $cT/2=0.50\ \mathrm m$, the horizontal displacement of the clock is $vT/2=0.8\times0.50\ \mathrm m=0.40\ \mathrm m$, and the vertical side is $d=0.30\ \mathrm m$, so

$$ d^2+\left(\frac{vT}{2}\right)^2=0.30^2+0.40^2=0.09+0.16=0.25=0.50^2=\left(\frac{cT}{2}\right)^2 $$

which holds (it is a $3:4:5$ right triangle). (d) Q's clock is at rest relative to Q, so its period in Q's frame is the same $\Delta\tau=2.00\ \mathrm{ns}$. By the principle of relativity, P and Q are on an equal footing. From P's point of view, Q's clock is moving at speed $0.8c$, so by the same calculation as in (b), the period of Q's clock as measured by P is $\gamma\,\Delta\tau=3.33\ \mathrm{ns}$. That is, from Q's point of view P's clock runs slow, and from P's point of view Q's clock runs slow. This is not a contradiction, as was confirmed in Example 55.7.

∎

Exercise 55.10: The color of an approaching light source

Solution

(a) For an approaching source we replace $v\to-v$, so that $\nu'/\nu=\sqrt{(1+\beta)/(1-\beta)}$. Substituting $\beta=0.5$,

$$ \frac{\nu'}{\nu}=\sqrt{\frac{1+0.5}{1-0.5}}=\sqrt{\frac{1.5}{0.5}}=\sqrt3=1.732 $$

Since the wavelength is inversely proportional to the frequency ($c=\nu\lambda$),

$$ \lambda'=\frac{\lambda}{\nu'/\nu}=\frac{500\ \mathrm{nm}}{1.732}=288.7\ \mathrm{nm}\approx289\ \mathrm{nm} $$

so visible green light is observed as ultraviolet light. (In terms of frequency, $\nu=c/\lambda=2.998\times10^{8}/(500\times10^{-9})=5.996\times10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}$ becomes $\nu'=1.732\nu=1.039\times10^{15}\ \mathrm{Hz}$.) (b) With the classical formula, $\nu'/\nu=1/(1-\beta)=1/0.5=2$, so

$$ \lambda''=\frac{500\ \mathrm{nm}}{2}=250\ \mathrm{nm} $$

The difference from (a) is $288.7-250=38.7\ \mathrm{nm}$, and the relativistic formula gives the longer wavelength. This is due to the time dilation factor $1/\gamma=\sqrt{1-\beta^2}=0.866$, which the classical formula does not contain; indeed $2\times0.866=1.732$, which agrees with the value in (a). (c) For a receding source, $\nu'/\nu=\sqrt{(1-\beta)/(1+\beta)}=\sqrt{0.5/1.5}=1/\sqrt3=0.577$, so

$$ \lambda'=\sqrt3\times500\ \mathrm{nm}=866\ \mathrm{nm} $$

which is near-infrared light. Comparing approach and recession at the same speed, the wavelengths are $0.577$ times and $1.732$ times $500\ \mathrm{nm}$, and these two factors multiply to $1$ ($\sqrt{(1+\beta)/(1-\beta)}\times\sqrt{(1-\beta)/(1+\beta)}=1$).

∎

← Back to Chapter 55, “Lorentz Contraction, Time Dilation, and the Transformation of Velocities”

Chapter 56 Minkowski Spacetime — Relativity of Simultaneity and Proper Time

Exercise 56.1: Velocity of a particle moving transversely

Solution

The velocity transformation law (Theorem 55.4 of Chapter 55; here we write $v$ for the speed of the frame and $u$ for the velocity of the particle) is, with $\beta=v/c=0.3$ and $\sqrt{1-\beta^2}=\sqrt{1-0.09}=\sqrt{0.91}=0.95394$,

$$ u'_x=\frac{u_x-v}{1-vu_x/c^2},\qquad u'_y=\frac{u_y\sqrt{1-\beta^2}}{1-vu_x/c^2} $$

Since $u_x=0$, the denominator is $1-0=1$, and so

$$ u'_x=-v=-0.3c,\qquad u'_y=0.4c\times0.95394=0.38158c\approx0.382c,\qquad u'_z=0 $$

The speed is $u'=c\sqrt{0.3^2+0.38158^2}=c\sqrt{0.09+0.1456}=c\sqrt{0.2356}=0.48539c\approx0.485c$ (we used $u'_y{}^2=0.4^2\times0.91=0.1456$). Verification: the left-hand side of the identity (Theorem 55.5 of Chapter 55) is $1-u'^2/c^2=1-0.2356=0.7644$, and the right-hand side is $(1-\beta^2)(1-u^2/c^2)/(1-\beta u_x/c)^2=0.91\times(1-0.16)/1=0.91\times0.84=0.7644$, so the two agree.

The direction of travel seen in frame S′ has an $x'$ component of $-0.3c$ and a $y'$ component of $0.38158c$, so it is tilted from the $y'$ axis toward the $-x'$ side, with $\tan\alpha=0.3/0.38158=0.7862$, that is, $\alpha=38.2^\circ$. Under the Galilean transformation we would have $u'_y=0.4c$ and $\tan\alpha=0.3/0.4=0.75$ ($\alpha=36.9^\circ$); in relativity, $u'_y$ is shrunk by the factor $\sqrt{1-\beta^2}$, and the tilt becomes slightly larger.

Exercise 56.2: Integrating the proper time

Solution

(a) For a constant speed $v$, $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-v^2/c^2}$ is a constant independent of time, so

$$ \tau=\int_0^T\frac{\dd t}{\gamma}=\frac{1}{\gamma}\int_0^T\dd t=\frac{T}{\gamma}=T\sqrt{1-\frac{v^2}{c^2}} $$

(the integral of a constant: $\int_0^T\dd t=T$). (b) For $v=0.99c$, $\sqrt{1-0.99^2}=\sqrt{0.0199}=0.14107$. Taking $T=1$ year $=365.25$ days, $\tau=0.14107\times365.25\ \text{days}=51.5$ days.
