That is, while one year passes on Earth, the spacecraft's clock advances by only about $52$ days. (c) Use the result of (a) on each interval and add. For $\gamma$, in the first half $1/\gamma=\sqrt{1-0.6^2}=0.8$ and in the second half $1/\gamma=\sqrt{1-0.8^2}=0.6$, so

$$ \tau=\int_0^{T/2}0.8\,\dd t+\int_{T/2}^{T}0.6\,\dd t=0.8\times\frac T2+0.6\times\frac T2=0.4T+0.3T=0.7\,T $$

This lies between the values obtained if the whole time $T$ were spent traveling at one speed or the other ($0.8T$ and $0.6T$).

Exercise 56.3: Frame independence of the invariant interval and the proper time

Solution

(a) The transformation formulas for infinitesimal quantities (Theorem 56.1 of Chapter 56) are $c\,\dd t'=\gamma(c\,\dd t-\beta\,\dd x)$, $\dd x'=\gamma(\dd x-\beta c\,\dd t)$, $\dd y'=\dd y$, $\dd z'=\dd z$. For the $t,x$ part,

$$ c^2\dd t'^2-\dd x'^2=\gamma^2\left\{(c\,\dd t-\beta\,\dd x)^2-(\dd x-\beta c\,\dd t)^2\right\}=\gamma^2\left\{(1-\beta^2)c^2\dd t^2-(1-\beta^2)\dd x^2\right\}=c^2\dd t^2-\dd x^2 $$

(on expanding, the cross terms $-2\beta c\,\dd t\,\dd x$ and $+2\beta c\,\dd t\,\dd x$ cancel each other, and $\gamma^2(1-\beta^2)=1$). Since $\dd y'^2+\dd z'^2=\dd y^2+\dd z^2$ clearly holds, we obtain $c^2\dd t'^2-\dd x'^2-\dd y'^2-\dd z'^2=c^2\dd t^2-\dd x^2-\dd y^2-\dd z^2$ also when $\dd y,\dd z$ are not zero, that is, when the particle (point mass) also moves in the $y,z$ directions.

(b) When the velocity of the particle in frame S is $\bm{u}$, $\dd\tau=\dd t\sqrt{1-u^2/c^2}$; when its velocity in frame S′ is $\bm{u}'$, $\dd\tau'=\dd t'\sqrt{1-u'^2/c^2}$. Taking the square root of the identity (Theorem 55.5 of Chapter 55) and setting $D=1-\beta u_x/c\gt0$, we get

$$ \sqrt{1-\frac{u'^2}{c^2}}=\frac{\sqrt{1-\beta^2}\,\sqrt{1-u^2/c^2}}{D}=\frac{1}{\gamma}\,\frac{\sqrt{1-u^2/c^2}}{D} $$

On the other hand, $\dd t'=\gamma(1-vu_x/c^2)\dd t=\gamma D\,\dd t$ (since $vu_x/c^2=\beta u_x/c$). Multiplying the two,

$$ \dd\tau'=\gamma D\,\dd t\times\frac{1}{\gamma}\frac{\sqrt{1-u^2/c^2}}{D}=\dd t\sqrt{1-u^2/c^2}=\dd\tau $$

Because $\gamma$ and $D$ cancel, $\dd\tau'=\dd\tau$; that is, the proper time does not depend on the frame.

Exercise 56.4: Additivity of rapidity (from the velocity addition law)

Solution

(a) Let $\phi_1=\tanh^{-1}\beta_1$ and $\phi_2=\tanh^{-1}\beta_2$; then $\beta_1=\tanh\phi_1$ and $\beta_2=\tanh\phi_2$. Substituting these into the right-hand side of the addition theorem for the hyperbolic tangent, $\tanh(\phi_1+\phi_2)=\dfrac{\tanh\phi_1+\tanh\phi_2}{1+\tanh\phi_1\tanh\phi_2}$, we obtain

$$ \tanh(\phi_1+\phi_2)=\frac{\beta_1+\beta_2}{1+\beta_1\beta_2}=\beta $$

Applying $\tanh^{-1}$ to both sides, $\tanh^{-1}\beta=\phi_1+\phi_2=\tanh^{-1}\beta_1+\tanh^{-1}\beta_2$. That is, the rapidity of the composite velocity is the sum of the two rapidities.

(b) $\beta_1=0.6$: $\phi_1=\frac12\ln\frac{1.6}{0.4}=\frac12\ln4=\ln2$. $\beta_2=0.8$: $\phi_2=\frac12\ln\frac{1.8}{0.2}=\frac12\ln9=\ln3$. The sum is $\phi=\ln2+\ln3=\ln6\approx1.792$. The composite velocity is $\beta=\tanh(\ln6)=\dfrac{6-1/6}{6+1/6}=\dfrac{35/6}{37/6}=\dfrac{35}{37}\approx0.9459$ ($\tanh x=(e^x-e^{-x})/(e^x+e^{-x})$). A direct computation gives $\beta=\dfrac{0.6+0.8}{1+0.6\times0.8}=\dfrac{1.4}{1.48}=\dfrac{35}{37}\approx0.9459$, which agrees.

Exercise 56.5: The light cone and the classification of events

Solution

(a) Compute $s^2=(ct)^2-x^2$. $\mathrm{P}_1$ $(4,3)$: $s^2=16-9=7\gt0$ (timelike). $\mathrm{P}_2$ $(3,5)$: $s^2=9-25=-16\lt0$ (spacelike). $\mathrm{P}_3$ $(5,5)$: $s^2=25-25=0$ (lightlike). The events that O can cause are $\mathrm{P}_1$, which is timelike and later than O, and $\mathrm{P}_3$, which is lightlike and later than O (light emitted from O reaches it). $\mathrm{P}_2$ is spacelike and cannot be reached from O even by light, so O cannot be its cause.

(b) $\mathrm{P}_1$: $\beta_0=x/(ct)=3/4$, $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-9/16}=4/\sqrt7$. In this frame, $x'=\gamma(x-\beta_0ct)=\gamma(3-\tfrac34\times4)=0$ (the same place), $ct'=\gamma(ct-\beta_0x)=\frac{4}{\sqrt7}(4-\tfrac94)=\frac{4}{\sqrt7}\times\frac74=\sqrt7\approx2.646\ \mathrm m$. That is, in the frame of speed $0.75c$ the two events occur at the same place, and the time difference is $\sqrt7\ \mathrm m/c\approx8.8\ \mathrm{ns}$ ($\sqrt{s^2}/c$). $\mathrm{P}_2$: $\beta_0=ct/x=3/5=0.6$, $\gamma=1.25$. $ct'=\gamma(ct-\beta_0x)=1.25\times(3-0.6\times5)=0$ (simultaneous), $x'=\gamma(x-\beta_0ct)=1.25\times(5-0.6\times3)=1.25\times3.2=4\ \mathrm m$. That is, the two events are simultaneous in the frame of speed $0.6c$, and the distance between them is $\sqrt{-s^2}=\sqrt{16}=4\ \mathrm m$.

(c) In a spacetime diagram (horizontal axis $x$, vertical axis $ct$) with O as the origin, draw the two straight lines $ct=\pm x$ at $45^\circ$ (the light cone). $\mathrm{P}_1$ $(x,ct)=(3,4)$ is drawn inside the light cone (on the future side), $\mathrm{P}_3$ $(5,5)$ on the light cone (on the upper-right $45^\circ$ line), and $\mathrm{P}_2$ $(5,3)$ outside the light cone (on the right side). The physical differences are as follows. O and $\mathrm{P}_1$ can be joined by a particle of speed $3/4\,c=0.75c$ (the speed needed from O to $\mathrm{P}_1$ is $x/(ct)=0.75$), so O can be a cause of $\mathrm{P}_1$. As for the temporal order, O comes first in every frame. On the other hand, joining O and $\mathrm{P}_2$ requires a speed $x/(ct)=5/3\,c\gt c$, and no such signal or object exists. The temporal order of $\mathrm{P}_2$ and O is reversed depending on the frame (if $\beta$ is made larger than $0.6$, $\mathrm{P}_2$ comes before O), but since there is no causal relation, there is no contradiction.

Exercise 56.6: The twin paradox and the asymmetry

Solution

(a) The round trip of $6$ light-years is traveled at $v=0.6c$, so it takes $T=6/0.6=10$ years in Earth time. The elder brother ages $10$ years. For the younger brother, $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-0.36}=1.25$ gives $\tau_{\mathrm B}=T/\gamma=10\times0.8=8$ years. Let us also check this with the invariant interval. For one leg, the difference between the two events is $(c\Delta t,\Delta x)=(5,3)$ (in units of years and light-years), so $\Delta s=\sqrt{25-9}=4$ years, and $8$ years for the round trip. The younger brother is $2$ years younger than the elder brother.

(b) The turnaround (event T) is, in frame S, at $x_{\mathrm T}=3$ light-years and $t_{\mathrm T}=3/0.6=5$ years. In the younger brother's inertial frame of reference on the outbound leg, the Earth event Q that is simultaneous with T satisfies, from $\Delta t=v\Delta x/c^2$ (with $c=1$ in units of years and light-years), $t_{\mathrm T}-t_{\mathrm Q}=0.6\times3=1.8$ years, so $t_{\mathrm Q}=5-1.8=3.2$ years. In the younger brother's inertial frame on the return leg (velocity $-0.6c$), $t_{\mathrm Q}-5=0.6\times3=1.8$ years, so $t_{\mathrm Q}=6.8$ years. Across the turnaround, the line of simultaneity jumps from Earth time $3.2$ years to $6.8$ years, a jump of $3.6$ years. Verification: during the younger brother's $4$ years on the outbound leg, the elder brother's clock advances $0.8\times4=3.2$ years, and it advances $3.2$ years on the return leg as well. Adding the jump of $3.6$ years, $3.2+3.6+3.2=10$ years, which agrees with the elder brother's age.

(c) The argument that “from the younger brother's point of view, the elder brother is the one moving” is correct as an argument about the period during which the younger brother stays in a single inertial frame (the outbound leg only, or the return leg only), and from the younger brother's point of view the elder brother's clock runs at $1/\gamma=0.8$ times the rate. However, at the turnaround the younger brother switches from the inertial frame of the outbound leg to that of the return leg. Across this switch, the Earth time that corresponds to the younger brother's “now” jumps from $3.2$ years to $6.8$ years. This jump is due to the relativity of simultaneity. The elder brother stays in a single inertial frame from beginning to end, and there is no such jump. Therefore the elder and younger brothers are not on an equal footing, and the proper time of the younger brother, who switched inertial frames (whose world line bends), is the shorter one. In the spacetime diagram, the elder brother's world line is the straight line joining the two events, and the proper time along a straight line is the maximum (Theorem 56.6). The younger brother can also tell that he is not in an inertial frame because he feels acceleration at the turnaround.

Exercise 56.7: Checking the twins' age difference by counting light signals

Solution

Take units of years and light-years ($c=1$); $\beta=0.6$, $\gamma=1.25$, the distance to the star is $3$ light-years, and the younger brother turns around at $5$ years of Earth time ($4$ years on his clock).

(a) Signals from the younger brother's clock at the end of his $n$th year. Outbound leg ($n=1,2,3,4$; we include $n=4$, the moment of turnaround): the younger brother's event occurs at Earth time $t=\gamma n=1.25n$ and position $x=0.6t=0.75n$. This signal reaches Earth at time $t+x=2n$ years. That is, at $2,4,6,8$ years, an interval of $2$ years (the younger brother's $1$-year interval is stretched to $2$ years for the elder brother). Return leg ($n=5,6,7,8$): with $m=n-4$, $t=5+1.25m$ and $x=3-0.6\times1.25m=3-0.75m$. The signal arrives at $t+x=8+0.5m$ years: $8.5,9,9.5,10$ years, an interval of $0.5$ years. The elder brother receives the first four signals at $2$-year intervals (years $2\to8$) and the remaining four at $0.5$-year intervals (years $8\to10$), for a total of $8$ signals, the last of which arrives at the reunion at year $10$.

(b) The signal that the elder brother sends at Earth time $n$ years ($n=1,\dots,10$) is light along $x=t-n$. A signal reaches the younger brother while he is on the outbound leg ($x=0.6t$, $0\le t\le5$) when $t-n=0.6t$, that is, at $t=n/0.4=2.5n$. The condition $t\le5$ gives $n=1,2$: at $2.5,\,5.0$ years of Earth time, and at $2.0,\,4.0$ years on the younger brother's clock ($\tau=t/\gamma=0.8t$). The interval is $2$ years. On the return leg ($x=3-0.6(t-5)=6-0.6t$), from $t-n=6-0.6t$ we get $t=(6+n)/1.6$. For $n=3,\dots,10$, $t=5.625,\,6.25,\,\dots,\,10$, and on the younger brother's clock $\tau=4+0.8(t-5)=4.5,\,5.0,\,\dots,\,8.0$. The interval is $0.5$ years. The younger brother receives $2$ signals on the outbound leg ($2$-year intervals) and $8$ signals on the return leg ($0.5$-year intervals) (the signal at the moment of turnaround, $\tau=4$, was counted on the outbound leg).

(c) The elder brother received $8$ signals from the younger brother, who sent them at intervals of $1$ year, so the younger brother's trip took $8$ years on his own clock. The younger brother received $2+8=10$ signals from the elder brother, who sent them at intervals of $1$ year, so the elder brother has aged $10$ years. The result that the elder brother has aged $10$ years and the younger brother $8$ years was thus confirmed by a method that merely counts light signals and does not use simultaneity. The reception intervals are $2$ years on the outbound leg and $0.5$ years on the return leg, a ratio of $4$ (the square of the Doppler factor $\sqrt{(1+\beta)/(1-\beta)}=2$), and this is the same whether seen from the elder brother's side or from the younger brother's.

Exercise 56.8: Relative velocity of head-on beams

Solution

(a) The velocity addition law: as seen from the rest frame of one particle (velocity $+0.9c$), the speed of the other (velocity $-0.9c$) would be $1.8c$ in the Galilean treatment, but by the addition law,

$$ \beta=\frac{0.9+0.9}{1+0.9\times0.9}=\frac{1.8}{1.81}=0.99448 $$

which is about $99.4\ \%$ of the speed of light. Now we find it using rapidity. $\phi=\tanh^{-1}0.9=\frac12\ln\frac{1.9}{0.1}=\frac12\ln19\approx1.4722$. Since the rapidity is $+\phi$ for one particle and $-\phi$ for the other, the rapidity of the relative velocity is the difference, $2\phi=2.9444$ (Theorem 56.7). Then $\beta=\tanh(2\phi)=\dfrac{e^{2\phi}-e^{-2\phi}}{e^{2\phi}+e^{-2\phi}}=\dfrac{19-1/19}{19+1/19}=\dfrac{360}{362}=\dfrac{180}{181}=0.99448$ (we used $e^{2\phi}=19$). This agrees with the result of the addition law, $1.8/1.81=180/181$.

(b) $\gamma_{\mathrm{rel}}=1/\sqrt{1-\beta^2}$, and since $1-\beta^2=1-\dfrac{180^2}{181^2}=\dfrac{181^2-180^2}{181^2}=\dfrac{361}{181^2}$, we have $\sqrt{1-\beta^2}=19/181$ and $\gamma_{\mathrm{rel}}=181/19\approx9.526$.

(c) In the laboratory, $\gamma=\cosh\phi=1/\sqrt{1-0.81}=1/\sqrt{0.19}$, so $\gamma^2=1/0.19=5.263$. Then $\cosh2\phi=2\cosh^2\phi-1=2\times5.263-1=9.526$. This agrees with $\gamma_{\mathrm{rel}}=\cosh2\phi=9.526$ from (b) (we applied $\gamma=\cosh\phi$ of Formula 56.2 to the rapidity of the relative velocity, $2\phi$). Colliding two $0.9c$ beams head-on is equivalent, in the rest frame of one particle, to a particle with $\gamma\approx9.5$ striking a particle at rest.

← Back to Chapter 56, “Minkowski Spacetime — Relativity of Simultaneity and Proper Time”

Chapter 57 Four-Vectors and Relativistic Dynamics — E=mc² and the Kinematics of Particles

Exercise 57.1: The spacetime interval $x\cdot x$

Solution

(a) Substituting $x^\mu=(ct,x,0,0)$ into the definition of the inner product $x\cdot x=(x^0)^2-(x^1)^2-(x^2)^2-(x^3)^2$ gives

$$ x\cdot x=(ct)^2-x^2-0^2-0^2=c^2t^2-x^2 $$

(b) Since all the components are in $\mathrm{m}$, the unit of $x\cdot x$ is $\mathrm{m^2}$.

$$ \begin{aligned} (ct,x)=(4,1):&\quad x\cdot x=4^2-1^2=15\ \mathrm{m^2}\gt0\quad\text{(timelike)}\\ (ct,x)=(1,4):&\quad x\cdot x=1^2-4^2=-15\ \mathrm{m^2}\lt0\quad\text{(spacelike)}\\ (ct,x)=(3,3):&\quad x\cdot x=3^2-3^2=0\quad\text{(lightlike)} \end{aligned} $$

(c) In the inertial frame S′ in which the event $(4,1)$ and the event at the origin occur at the same place, $x'^1=0$, so $x'\cdot x'=(ct')^2$. Since the inner product is invariant (Theorem 57.1), $x'\cdot x'=x\cdot x=15\ \mathrm{m^2}$, and $ct'=\sqrt{15}\ \mathrm{m}=3.873\ \mathrm{m}$. Therefore the proper time is

$$ \Delta\tau=\frac{\sqrt{15}\ \mathrm{m}}{3.00\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s}}=1.29\times10^{-8}\ \mathrm{s}\quad(12.9\ \mathrm{ns}) $$

Check: this frame S′ is the frame in which the origin and the event are regarded as being at the same place, so it moves relative to frame S with velocity $v=x/t=c\times1/4$, that is, $\beta=0.25$, and $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-1/16}=4/\sqrt{15}$. By the Lorentz transformation, $ct'=\gamma(ct-\beta x)=\dfrac{4}{\sqrt{15}}(4-0.25\times1)=\dfrac{4\times3.75}{\sqrt{15}}=\dfrac{15}{\sqrt{15}}=\sqrt{15}\ \mathrm{m}$, which agrees. Also, $x'=\gamma(x-\beta ct)=\dfrac{4}{\sqrt{15}}(1-0.25\times4)=0$.

Exercise 57.2: $\Lambda^{\mathsf T}g\Lambda=g$ and the invariance of the inner product ($\beta=0.8$)

Solution

(a) Since $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-0.8^2}=1/0.6=5/3$ and $\beta\gamma=0.8\times5/3=4/3$,

$$ \Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}5/3&-4/3\\-4/3&5/3\end{pmatrix},\qquad g=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad g\Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}5/3&-4/3\\4/3&-5/3\end{pmatrix} $$

Since $\Lambda$ is a symmetric matrix, $\Lambda^{\mathsf T}=\Lambda$, and therefore

$$ \Lambda^{\mathsf T}g\Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}5/3&-4/3\\-4/3&5/3\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}5/3&-4/3\\4/3&-5/3\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{25}{9}-\frac{16}{9}&-\frac{20}{9}+\frac{20}{9}\\-\frac{20}{9}+\frac{20}{9}&\frac{16}{9}-\frac{25}{9}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&-1\end{pmatrix}=g $$

(b) From $a'^0=\gamma(a^0-\beta a^1)=\dfrac53(3-0.8\times1)=\dfrac53\times2.2=\dfrac{11}{3}$, $a'^1=\gamma(a^1-\beta a^0)=\dfrac53(1-0.8\times3)=\dfrac53\times(-1.4)=-\dfrac73$, $a'^2=2$, and $a'^3=0$, we obtain $a'^\mu=(11/3,\,-7/3,\,2,\,0)$. Similarly, from $b'^0=\dfrac53(2-0.8\times1)=2$, $b'^1=\dfrac53(1-0.8\times2)=\dfrac53\times(-0.6)=-1$, $b'^2=0$, and $b'^3=1$, we obtain $b'^\mu=(2,\,-1,\,0,\,1)$. The inner product is

$$ a'\cdot b'=\frac{11}{3}\times2-\left(-\frac73\right)(-1)-2\times0-0\times1=\frac{22}{3}-\frac73=\frac{15}{3}=5 $$

which agrees with the value in frame S, $a\cdot b=3\times2-(1\times1+2\times0+0\times1)=5$ (Theorem 57.1).

Exercise 57.3: The four-velocity and proper time

Solution

(a) Since $u^\mu=\gamma(c,\bm{v})$, we have $u\cdot u=(\gamma c)^2-\gamma^2\bm{v}\cdot\bm{v}=\gamma^2(c^2-v^2)$. Substituting $\gamma^2=1/(1-v^2/c^2)$ gives

$$ u\cdot u=\frac{c^2-v^2}{1-v^2/c^2}=\frac{c^2\left(1-v^2/c^2\right)}{1-v^2/c^2}=c^2 $$

which does not depend on the speed $v$. (b) If $v=0.8c$, then $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-0.64}=5/3$, and $u^0=\gamma c=\tfrac53c$, $u^1=\gamma v=\tfrac53\times0.8c=\tfrac43c$, $u^2=u^3=0$; that is, $u^\mu=(5/3,\ 4/3,\ 0,\ 0)\,c$. Check: $u\cdot u=(25/9-16/9)c^2=c^2$. (c) Since $\dd t/\dd\tau=u^0/c=\gamma=5/3$, $\Delta\tau=\Delta t/\gamma=30\ \mathrm{s}\times3/5=18\ \mathrm{s}$. A clock moving with the particle advances by only $18\ \mathrm{s}$ (time dilation).

Exercise 57.4: A particle at rest, the nonrelativistic limit, and a massless particle

Solution

(a) Since the particle is at rest, $\bm{v}=\bm{0}$ and $\gamma=1$, so $p^\mu=mu^\mu=m(c,\bm{0})=(mc,\bm{0})$. Comparing with $p^0=E/c$ gives $E=mc^2$, and the spatial components give $\bm{p}=\bm{0}$.

(b) Writing $E=\sqrt{(pc)^2+(mc^2)^2}=mc^2\sqrt{1+x}$ with $x=(p/mc)^2\ll1$ and using $\sqrt{1+x}=1+\tfrac12x-\tfrac18x^2+\cdots$, we obtain

$$ E\approx mc^2\left[1+\frac12\frac{p^2}{m^2c^2}-\frac18\frac{p^4}{m^4c^4}\right]=mc^2+\frac{p^2}{2m}-\frac{p^4}{8m^3c^2} $$

Keeping only the first two terms gives $E\approx mc^2+p^2/2m$. For an electron with $pc=0.05\ \mathrm{MeV}$, the exact value is $E=\sqrt{0.05^2+0.511^2}=\sqrt{0.263621}=0.513440\ \mathrm{MeV}$, so $T=E-mc^2=0.002440\ \mathrm{MeV}=2.44\ \mathrm{keV}$. On the other hand, $p^2/2m=(pc)^2/(2mc^2)=0.0025/1.022=0.002446\ \mathrm{MeV}$. The relative deviation is $0.002446/0.002440-1\approx0.24\%$, which is nearly equal to the correction $p^2/(4m^2c^2)=(0.05/0.511)^2/4=0.0024$ caused by the third term. Including the third term, we subtract $p^4/(8m^3c^2)=(pc)^4/[8(mc^2)^3]=6.25\times10^{-6}/(8\times0.1334)=5.86\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{MeV}$ to get $0.002446-0.000006=0.002440\ \mathrm{MeV}$, which agrees with the exact value.

(c) If $m=0$, then $E^2=(pc)^2$, and since $E\gt0$, $E=pc$. In this case $\beta=v/c=pc/E=1$, that is, $v=c$ (a massless particle moves at the speed of light).

Exercise 57.5: Mass and energy

Solution

(a) $E=mc^2=(1\ \mathrm{kg})(2.998\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s})^2=8.99\times10^{16}\ \mathrm{J}$. In TNT equivalent, this is $8.99\times10^{16}/(4.184\times10^9)\approx2.15\times10^7\ \mathrm{t}$, about $21.5$ megatons ($1\ \mathrm{Mt}=10^6\ \mathrm{t}$).

(b) The required quantity of heat is $Q=cm\Delta T=4186\ \mathrm{J/(kg\,K)}\times1\ \mathrm{kg}\times100\ \mathrm{K}=4.19\times10^5\ \mathrm{J}$, and since this is the increase $\Delta E$ in the energy of the water, the increase in mass is

$$ \Delta m=\frac{\Delta E}{c^2}=\frac{4.186\times10^5\ \mathrm{J}}{\left(2.998\times10^8\ \mathrm{m/s}\right)^2}=4.66\times10^{-12}\ \mathrm{kg} $$

(about $4.7\ \mathrm{ng}$; the fractional increase is $4.7\times10^{-12}$). In everyday exchanges of energy, the change in mass is too small to be measured.

(c) Expanding $\gamma=(1-\beta^2)^{-1/2}$ with $(1-x)^{-1/2}=1+\tfrac12x+\tfrac38x^2+\cdots$ ($x=\beta^2$) gives $\gamma-1=\tfrac12\beta^2+\tfrac38\beta^4+\cdots$. Since $\beta^2mc^2=mv^2$ and $\beta^4mc^2=mv^4/c^2$,

$$ T=(\gamma-1)mc^2=\frac12mv^2+\frac38\,\frac{mv^4}{c^2}+\cdots $$

The first term is the kinetic energy $\tfrac12mv^2$ of high school physics. When $v=0.05c$, $\beta^2=0.0025$, $\gamma-1=1.25235\times10^{-3}$, and $\tfrac12\beta^2=1.25\times10^{-3}$, so the relative deviation is $1.25235/1.25-1\approx1.9\times10^{-3}$, about $0.19\%$ (nearly equal to $\tfrac34\beta^2=0.1875\%$).

Exercise 57.6: Two-body decay $M\to m+m$ and $Z\to\mu^+\mu^-$

Solution

(a) In the rest frame, the four-momentum before the decay is $P^\mu=(Mc,\bm{0})$, and after the decay it is $p_1^\mu+p_2^\mu$. Taking the inner product of each side of $p_2^\mu=P^\mu-p_1^\mu$ with itself gives $p_2\cdot p_2=P\cdot P-2P\cdot p_1+p_1\cdot p_1$, and since $p_2\cdot p_2=p_1\cdot p_1=m^2c^2$, $P\cdot P=M^2c^2$, and $P\cdot p_1=Mc\cdot E_1/c=ME_1$,

$$ m^2c^2=M^2c^2-2ME_1+m^2c^2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad E_1=\frac{Mc^2}{2} $$

From conservation of energy, $E_1+E_2=Mc^2$, we get $E_2=Mc^2/2$, which shows that $E_1=E_2=Mc^2/2$. From $p^2c^2=E_1^2-m^2c^4=M^2c^4/4-m^2c^4$, the magnitude of the momentum is

$$ p=\frac{c}{2}\sqrt{M^2-4m^2} $$

(this requires $M\gt2m$).

(b) Taking $M=91.19\ \mathrm{GeV}/c^2$ and $m=m_\mu=105.658\ \mathrm{MeV}/c^2$, the energy is $E=Mc^2/2=45.595\ \mathrm{GeV}$. The momentum is $pc=\sqrt{E^2-(m_\mu c^2)^2}=\sqrt{45595^2-105.658^2}\ \mathrm{MeV}=45594.9\ \mathrm{MeV}\approx45.595\ \mathrm{GeV}$ (it differs from $E$ by only $0.12\ \mathrm{MeV}$), so $p\approx45.595\ \mathrm{GeV}/c$. Also, $\gamma=E/(m_\mu c^2)=45595/105.658\approx431.5$, so the muon travels at nearly the speed of light ($1-\beta\approx1/(2\gamma^2)=2.7\times10^{-6}$).

Exercise 57.7: The threshold energy of $\mathrm p+\mathrm p\to\mathrm p+\mathrm p+\pi^0$

Solution

(a) The sum of the masses after the reaction is $2m_{\mathrm p}+m_{\pi^0}$, and the threshold condition is $M_{\mathrm{inv}}c^2=(2m_{\mathrm p}+m_{\pi^0})c^2$. The invariant mass for a fixed target is $M_{\mathrm{inv}}^2c^4=2m_{\mathrm p}^2c^4+2Em_{\mathrm p}c^2$ (Example 57.22), so

$$ 2m_{\mathrm p}^2c^4+2Em_{\mathrm p}c^2=\left(2m_{\mathrm p}+m_{\pi^0}\right)^2c^4=\left(4m_{\mathrm p}^2+4m_{\mathrm p}m_{\pi^0}+m_{\pi^0}^2\right)c^4 $$

Solving this for $E$ gives $E=\left(m_{\mathrm p}+2m_{\pi^0}+\dfrac{m_{\pi^0}^2}{2m_{\mathrm p}}\right)c^2$, so the kinetic energy is $T=E-m_{\mathrm p}c^2=\left(2m_{\pi^0}+\dfrac{m_{\pi^0}^2}{2m_{\mathrm p}}\right)c^2$. Substituting the numerical values,

$$ T=2\times134.977+\frac{134.977^2}{2\times938.272}=269.954+\frac{18218.8}{1876.544}=269.954+9.709=279.66\ \mathrm{MeV}\approx280\ \mathrm{MeV} $$

(b) In the colliding-beam case, the total momentum is $0$ and $M_{\mathrm{inv}}c^2=2E$, so $2E=(2m_{\mathrm p}+m_{\pi^0})c^2$ and $E=m_{\mathrm p}c^2+\tfrac12m_{\pi^0}c^2$. Therefore the kinetic energy per beam is $T=\tfrac12m_{\pi^0}c^2=67.49\ \mathrm{MeV}$ ($135\ \mathrm{MeV}$ for the two beams).

(c) In the fixed-target case, the momentum carried by the beam proton does not disappear after the reaction, so the particles produced continue to move forward as a whole (the same situation as in Figure 57.7 of Section 57.8). Part of the kinetic energy is therefore used for the motion of the center of mass and cannot be used to create mass. To compensate, $T$ must be $280\ \mathrm{MeV}$, about twice the rest energy $135\ \mathrm{MeV}$ of the $\pi^0$. In the colliding-beam case, the total momentum is zero, and the total kinetic energy $2\times67.49=135\ \mathrm{MeV}$ is exactly the rest energy of the $\pi^0$.

Exercise 57.8: Backscattering of the gamma rays of $^{137}\mathrm{Cs}$

Solution

At $\theta=180^\circ$, $1-\cos\theta=2$, so from the energy formula for Compton scattering in Section 57.9, $E'=E/[1+(E/m_{\mathrm e}c^2)(1-\cos\theta)]$, we obtain

$$ E'=\frac{662\ \mathrm{keV}}{1+2\times662/511}=\frac{662}{1+2.591}\ \mathrm{keV}=\frac{662}{3.591}\ \mathrm{keV}=184.4\ \mathrm{keV}\approx184\ \mathrm{keV} $$

From conservation of energy, the kinetic energy received by the electron is $T_{\mathrm e}=E-E'=662-184.4=477.6\ \mathrm{keV}\approx478\ \mathrm{keV}$, and this is the maximum energy the electron can receive in the scattering (the Compton edge). The direction of the electron is determined by conservation of momentum, $\bm{p}_{\mathrm e}'=\bm{q}-\bm{q}'$. In backscattering the scattered photon travels opposite to the incident direction ($\bm{q}'=-(E'/c)\bm{n}$), so $\bm{p}_{\mathrm e}'=[(E+E')/c]\,\bm{n}$; that is, the electron flies in the same direction as the incident photon ($\phi=0^\circ$). The magnitude of the momentum is $p_{\mathrm e}c=E+E'=846.4\ \mathrm{keV}$, and the energy is $\sqrt{846.4^2+511^2}=989\ \mathrm{keV}=511+477.6\ \mathrm{keV}$, which is consistent with conservation of energy. As another check, we compute with wavelengths: $\lambda=hc/E=1239.84\ \mathrm{keV\,pm}/662\ \mathrm{keV}=1.873\ \mathrm{pm}$ and $\lambda'=\lambda+2\lambda_{\mathrm C}=1.873+4.853=6.726\ \mathrm{pm}$, so $E'=1239.84/6.726=184.3\ \mathrm{keV}$, which agrees.

← Back to Chapter 57, “Four-Vectors and Relativistic Dynamics — E=mc² and the Kinematics of Particles”

Chapter 58 Natural Units and the Four-Vector Formulation of Electromagnetism — Preparing for Particle Physics

Exercise 58.1: Converting natural units

Solution

(a) The energy corresponding to a length $\ell$ is $E=\hbar c/\ell$. With $\ell=0.84\ \mathrm{fm}$,

$$ E=\frac{197.327\ \mathrm{MeV\,fm}}{0.84\ \mathrm{fm}}=2.35\times10^{2}\ \mathrm{MeV} $$

(here $197.327/0.84=234.9$). The world at the size of the proton lies at an energy scale of a few hundred $\mathrm{MeV}$.

(b) In natural units, $1\ \mathrm{GeV}^{-1}$ becomes the length $\hbar c/(1\ \mathrm{GeV})$ when multiplied by $\hbar c$, and the time $\hbar/(1\ \mathrm{GeV})$ when multiplied by $\hbar$ (Formula 58.1). Using $1\ \mathrm{GeV}=10^{3}\ \mathrm{MeV}$, we obtain

$$ \frac{\hbar c}{1\ \mathrm{GeV}}=\frac{197.327\ \mathrm{MeV\,fm}}{10^{3}\ \mathrm{MeV}}=0.197\,327\ \mathrm{fm}=1.973\times10^{-16}\ \mathrm{m},\qquad \frac{\hbar}{1\ \mathrm{GeV}}=\frac{6.582\,12\times10^{-22}\ \mathrm{MeV\,s}}{10^{3}\ \mathrm{MeV}}=6.582\times10^{-25}\ \mathrm{s} $$

(c) Since this is a quantity with the dimension of an area, we square the length $1\ \mathrm{GeV}^{-1}$: $(0.197\,327\ \mathrm{fm})^2=0.038\,94\ \mathrm{fm^2}$. Because $1\ \mathrm{fm^2}=10^{-30}\ \mathrm{m^2}=10\ \mathrm{mb}$ (since $1\ \mathrm{mb}=10^{-31}\ \mathrm{m^2}$, we have $10^{-30}/10^{-31}=10$),

$$ 1\ \mathrm{GeV}^{-2}=0.038\,94\times10\ \mathrm{mb}=0.389\ \mathrm{mb} $$

Conversely, $1\ \mathrm{mb}$ is about $2.57\ \mathrm{GeV}^{-2}$.

(d) The SI unit of $\hbar c$ is $[\mathrm{J\,s}]\cdot[\mathrm{m/s}]=\mathrm{J\,m}$ (energy × length). In natural units $\hbar c=1$, so “energy × length $=1$”; that is, length $=1/$energy, and the dimension of length is “energy$^{-1}$”. Similarly, from the unit of $\hbar$, $\mathrm{J\,s}$, we get time $=\hbar/$energy, so the dimension of time is also “energy$^{-1}$”.

∎

Exercise 58.2: Computing the lifetime of the muon in natural units

Solution

(a) We have $G_{\mathrm F}^2=(1.166\,379\times10^{-5})^2\ \mathrm{GeV}^{-4}=1.3604\times10^{-10}\ \mathrm{GeV}^{-4}$, $m_\mu^5=(0.105\,658)^5\ \mathrm{GeV}^5=1.3168\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{GeV}^5$, and $192\pi^3=192\times31.006=5953.2$. Therefore

$$ \Gamma=\frac{1.3604\times10^{-10}\times1.3168\times10^{-5}}{5953.2}\ \mathrm{GeV}=\frac{1.7914\times10^{-15}}{5953.2}\ \mathrm{GeV}=3.009\times10^{-19}\ \mathrm{GeV} $$

(since $\mathrm{GeV}^{-4}\times\mathrm{GeV}^{5}=\mathrm{GeV}$, the result has the dimension of energy; it corresponds to $3.009\times10^{-10}\ \mathrm{eV}$).

(b) In $\tau=\hbar/\Gamma$, substituting $\hbar=6.582\,12\times10^{-25}\ \mathrm{GeV\,s}$ gives

$$ \tau=\frac{6.582\,12\times10^{-25}\ \mathrm{GeV\,s}}{3.009\times10^{-19}\ \mathrm{GeV}}=2.187\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{s} $$

The difference from the measured value $2.197\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{s}$ is about $0.45\%$, which nearly matches the difference caused by this simple formula's neglect of the effect of the electron mass (about $0.02\%$) and of the radiative correction of quantum electrodynamics (which reduces the decay width by about $0.4\%$). Merely by calculating with the formula in natural units, we obtain a lifetime of order $10^{-6}\ \mathrm{s}$ with three significant figures.

(c) Noting that $G_{\mathrm F}/(\hbar c)^3$ is a quantity measured in $\mathrm{GeV}^{-2}$, and making the replacement $m_\mu\to m_\mu c^2$ for the mass, we obtain

$$ \Gamma=\frac{1}{192\pi^3}\left[\frac{G_{\mathrm F}}{(\hbar c)^3}\right]^2(m_\mu c^2)^5 $$

(dimensions: $[\mathrm{GeV}^{-2}]^2[\mathrm{GeV}]^5=\mathrm{GeV}$). The lifetime is $\tau=\hbar/\Gamma$, and if $G_{\mathrm F}$ is given in SI units ($\mathrm{J\,m^3}$), then $\Gamma=G_{\mathrm F}^2(m_\mu c^2)^5/[192\pi^3(\hbar c)^6]$ (dimensions: $(\mathrm{J\,m^3})^2\mathrm{J}^5/(\mathrm{J\,m})^6=\mathrm{J}$).

∎

Exercise 58.3: Finding the electric field from the transformation of the potentials

Solution

(a) In frame S, $A^0=\varphi/c=-E_0x/c$ and $A^1=A_x=0$. To write the result in the coordinates of frame S′, we use the inverse transformation $x=\gamma(x'+\beta ct')$. From $\beta=0.5$, we get $\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-0.25}=2/\sqrt3$, $\gamma^2=4/3$, $\gamma^2\beta=2/3$, and $\gamma^2\beta^2=1/3$. Using the same transformation as for $x^\mu$, namely $A'^0=\gamma(A^0-\beta A^1)$ and $A'^1=\gamma(A^1-\beta A^0)$, we obtain

$$ A'^0=\gamma A^0=-\frac{\gamma E_0}{c}\,x=-\frac{\gamma^2E_0}{c}\,(x'+\beta ct'),\qquad A'^1=-\gamma\beta A^0=\frac{\gamma\beta E_0}{c}\,x=\frac{\gamma^2\beta E_0}{c}\,(x'+\beta ct') $$

Therefore $\varphi'=cA'^0$, $A'_x=A'^1$, and $A'_y=A'_z=0$, and substituting the numerical values gives

$$ \varphi'=-\frac43E_0\left(x'+\frac{ct'}2\right),\qquad A'_x=\frac23\,\frac{E_0}{c}\left(x'+\frac{ct'}2\right) $$

(dimensions: $E_0x'\ [\mathrm V]$ has the dimension of electric potential, and $E_0x'/c\ [\mathrm{V\,s/m}]$ has the dimension of $\bm A$).

(b) The gradient of the electric potential is $\partial_{x'}\varphi'=-\dfrac43E_0$, and the time derivative of the vector potential is $\partial_{t'}A'_x=\dfrac23\dfrac{E_0}{c}\cdot\dfrac c2=\dfrac13E_0$, so

$$ E'_x=-\pdiff{\varphi'}{x'}-\pdiff{A'_x}{t'}=\frac43E_0-\frac13E_0=E_0 $$

Also, $E'_y=-\partial_{y'}\varphi'-\partial_{t'}A'_y=0$ and $E'_z=0$. Furthermore, for the curl of $\bm A'=(A'_x(t',x'),0,0)$ we have $(\rot'\bm A')_y=\partial_{z'}A'_x-\partial_{x'}A'_z=0$, $(\rot'\bm A')_z=\partial_{x'}A'_y-\partial_{y'}A'_x=0$, and $(\rot'\bm A')_x=0$, so $\bm B'=\bm0$. This agrees with the result obtained by setting $E_y=E_z=0$, $\bm B=\bm0$ in Theorem 58.5, namely $E'_x=E_x=E_0$, $\bm E'_\perp=\bm0$, $\bm B'=\bm0$. An electric field parallel to the direction of motion is unchanged even for a moving observer. Note also that $\gamma^2=4/3$ appears in the electric potential and $\gamma^2\beta^2=1/3$ in the time variation of $A'_x$, and that the difference $\gamma^2(1-\beta^2)=1$ remains; this illustrates well a property of the Lorentz transformation.

(c) Since $\dfrac1{c^2}\partial_{t'}\varphi'=\dfrac1{c^2}\left(-\dfrac43E_0\cdot\dfrac c2\right)=-\dfrac23\dfrac{E_0}c$ and $\partial_{x'}A'_x=\dfrac23\dfrac{E_0}c$,

$$ \partial'_\mu A'^\mu=\frac1{c^2}\pdiff{\varphi'}{t'}+\pdiff{A'_x}{x'}=-\frac23\frac{E_0}c+\frac23\frac{E_0}c=0 $$

so the Lorenz condition also holds in frame S′ (in frame S we had $\partial_\mu A^\mu=\dfrac1{c^2}\partial_t\varphi+\nabla\cdot\bm A=0+0=0$; this is an example of the covariance of the Lorenz condition).

∎

Exercise 58.4: The field-strength tensor and invariants of a uniform magnetic field

Solution

(a) We have $A^0=\varphi/c=0$, $A^1=-B_0y/2$, $A^2=B_0x/2$, $A^3=0$, $\partial^0=\dfrac1c\partial_t$, and $\partial^i=-\partial_i$. The time derivative is zero (the field does not depend on time), and $A^0=0$, so $F^{0i}=\partial^0A^i-\partial^iA^0=0$. The spatial components are

$$ F^{12}=\partial^1A^2-\partial^2A^1=-\pdiff{A^2}{x}+\pdiff{A^1}{y}=-\frac{B_0}{2}+\left(-\frac{B_0}{2}\right)=-B_0 $$

Moreover, $F^{13}=-\partial_xA^3+\partial_zA^1=0$ and $F^{23}=-\partial_yA^3+\partial_zA^2=0$. From antisymmetry, $F^{21}=+B_0$. Therefore

$$ F^{\mu\nu}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&0\\0&0&-B_0&0\\0&B_0&0&0\\0&0&0&0\end{pmatrix} $$

This agrees with the relation $F^{12}=-B_z$, with $B_z=B_0$ and all the other components being $0$. Also, $F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}=2B_0^2$.

(b) We have $\beta=0.6$, $\gamma=1.25$, and $v=0.6c$. Substituting $E_y=0$, $B_z=B_0$ into Theorem 58.5 gives

$$ E'_y=\gamma(E_y-vB_z)=-1.25\times0.6\times2.998\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{m/s}\times1.0\ \mathrm{T}=-2.25\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{V/m},\qquad B'_z=\gamma\left(B_z-\frac{v}{c^2}E_y\right)=1.25\ \mathrm{T} $$

Also, $E'_x=E'_z=0$ and $B'_x=B'_y=0$. The invariant is

$$ E'^2-c^2B'^2=(2.248\times10^{8})^2-(2.998\times10^{8}\times1.25)^2=5.06\times10^{16}-1.404\times10^{17}=-8.99\times10^{16}\ \mathrm{V^2/m^2} $$

which agrees with $-c^2B_0^2=-(2.998\times10^{8}\times1.0)^2=-8.99\times10^{16}\ \mathrm{V^2/m^2}$. An observer moving through a uniform magnetic field observes a strong electric field (in this example, $2.2\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{V/m}$) and a slightly strengthened magnetic field.

∎

Exercise 58.5: Components of $\partial_\mu F^{\mu\nu}=\mu_0j^\nu$ and conservation of charge

Solution

(a) The equation for $\nu=2$ is $\partial_0F^{02}+\partial_1F^{12}+\partial_2F^{22}+\partial_3F^{32}=\mu_0j^2$. From the matrix of the field-strength tensor, $F^{02}=-E_y/c$, $F^{12}=-B_z$, $F^{22}=0$, and $F^{32}=B_x$, so

$$ -\frac1{c^2}\pdiff{E_y}{t}-\pdiff{B_z}{x}+\pdiff{B_x}{z}=\mu_0j_y $$

Since $\partial_zB_x-\partial_xB_z=(\rot\bm B)_y$, we obtain $(\rot\bm B)_y=\mu_0j_y+\dfrac1{c^2}\pdiff{E_y}{t}$.

(b) In the equation $\partial_\mu F^{\mu\nu}=\mu_0j^\nu$, apply $\partial_\nu$ to both sides and sum over $\nu$ from $0$ to $3$:

$$ \partial_\nu\partial_\mu F^{\mu\nu}=\mu_0\,\partial_\nu j^\nu $$

The value of the left-hand side does not change when the names of the summed indices $\mu,\nu$ are interchanged, so $\partial_\nu\partial_\mu F^{\mu\nu}=\partial_\mu\partial_\nu F^{\nu\mu}$. By the antisymmetry of $F$, $F^{\nu\mu}=-F^{\mu\nu}$, the chain continues as $=-\partial_\mu\partial_\nu F^{\mu\nu}$, and by interchanging the order of partial differentiation, $\partial_\mu\partial_\nu=\partial_\nu\partial_\mu$, it continues as $=-\partial_\nu\partial_\mu F^{\mu\nu}$. This is equal to the original left-hand side with its sign reversed, so it is $0$. Therefore $\partial_\nu j^\nu=0$, that is, $\dfrac1c\partial_t(c\rho)+\nabla\cdot\bm j=\partial_t\rho+\nabla\cdot\bm j=0$ (conservation of charge).

∎

Exercise 58.6: A plane electromagnetic wave as seen by a moving observer

Solution

(a) In frame S, $E_y=E_0$, $B_z=E_0/c$, and the other components are $0$. With $\beta=0.6$ and $\gamma=1.25$, and letting $v=\beta c$, Theorem 58.5 gives

$$ E'_y=\gamma(E_y-vB_z)=\gamma\left(E_0-\beta c\cdot\frac{E_0}{c}\right)=\gamma(1-\beta)E_0=1.25\times0.4\times1.0\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{V/m}=5.0\times10^{2}\ \mathrm{V/m} $$ $$ B'_z=\gamma\left(B_z-\frac{v}{c^2}E_y\right)=\gamma\left(\frac{E_0}{c}-\frac{\beta E_0}{c}\right)=\gamma(1-\beta)\frac{E_0}{c}=\frac{5.0\times10^{2}\ \mathrm{V/m}}{2.998\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{m/s}}=1.67\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm{T} $$

and the other components remain $0$. The relation $E'_y/B'_z=c$, that is, $E'=cB'$, is preserved (a consequence of the invariant $E^2-c^2B^2=0$).

(b) We have $E'/E_0=\gamma(1-\beta)=\dfrac{1-\beta}{\sqrt{(1-\beta)(1+\beta)}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{1-\beta}{1+\beta}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{0.4}{1.6}}=0.5$, which is the same factor as $\omega'/\omega$ for the wave four-vector in Example 58.7. To an observer moving away together with the wave, the amplitude and the frequency both appear smaller by the same factor (this corresponds to the energy of a photon decreasing while the number of photons is preserved).

(c) If the velocity of S′ is in the $-x$ direction (the direction approaching the light source), then replacing $\beta\to-\beta$ gives $E'/E_0=\gamma(1+\beta)=\sqrt{\dfrac{1+\beta}{1-\beta}}=2$ and $E'=2.0\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{V/m}$ ($B'=E'/c$). The amplitude doubles, and the frequency also doubles (blueshift).

∎

Exercise 58.7: Trajectory radius of a cosmic-ray proton in the geomagnetic field

Solution

(a) From $p\ [\mathrm{GeV}/c]=0.2998\,B\ [\mathrm{T}]\,r\ [\mathrm{m}]$ in Formula 58.2,

$$ r=\frac{p}{0.2998\,B}=\frac{10}{0.2998\times5\times10^{-5}}\ \mathrm{m}=6.67\times10^{5}\ \mathrm{m}=6.7\times10^{2}\ \mathrm{km} $$

Compared with the Earth's radius, $6.4\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{km}$, this is about $1/10$ ($667/6400=0.104$), so the geomagnetic field bends cosmic-ray protons of around $10\ \mathrm{GeV}/c$ on a radius far smaller than the size of the Earth. This is the reason why the geomagnetic field keeps low-energy cosmic rays away from the Earth's surface.

(b) The trajectory radius $r=p/(qB)$ is determined only by the momentum and the magnitude $\abs{q}$ of the electric charge, and does not depend on the mass. An electron has the same magnitude of electric charge as a proton, so for the same momentum the radius is the same, $6.7\times10^{2}\ \mathrm{km}$ (since the sign of the charge is opposite, the direction of bending is opposite).
If instead the kinetic energy is the same, $T=10\ \mathrm{GeV}$, the momentum is determined by $pc=\sqrt{T^2+2Tmc^2}$. For the proton ($m_{\mathrm p}c^2=0.938\ \mathrm{GeV}$), $pc=\sqrt{100+18.77}\ \mathrm{GeV}=10.90\ \mathrm{GeV}$ and $r=10.90/(0.2998\times5\times10^{-5})\ \mathrm{m}=7.27\times10^{5}\ \mathrm{m}$. For the electron ($m_{\mathrm e}c^2=0.511\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{GeV}$), $pc=\sqrt{100+0.0102}\ \mathrm{GeV}=10.0005\ \mathrm{GeV}$ and $r=6.67\times10^{5}\ \mathrm{m}$, so the difference is only about $9\%$. When the energy is sufficiently high, the difference in mass has almost no effect (the ultrarelativistic limit, where $pc\approx T$).

∎

Exercise 58.8: Expansion of the Hamiltonian of a free particle

Solution

(a) We have $p=\partial L/\partial v=\gamma mv$ and $H=pv-L=\gamma mv^2+mc^2/\gamma=\gamma mc^2$ (main text, Section 58.9.2). We now write this in terms of $p$. Since $p^2c^2=\gamma^2m^2v^2c^2=\gamma^2m^2c^4\beta^2$ and $\gamma^2\beta^2=\gamma^2-1$ (from $\gamma^2(1-\beta^2)=1$), we have $p^2c^2=(\gamma^2-1)m^2c^4$, that is, $\gamma^2m^2c^4=p^2c^2+m^2c^4$. Taking the square root, with $\gamma mc^2\gt0$, we obtain

$$ H=\gamma mc^2=\sqrt{p^2c^2+m^2c^4} $$

This expresses the Hamiltonian in terms of the momentum.

(b) Writing $H=mc^2\sqrt{1+x}$ with $x=p^2/(mc)^2$, for $p\ll mc$ we have $x\ll1$. From $\sqrt{1+x}=1+\dfrac x2-\dfrac{x^2}8+\cdots$ (Taylor expansion),

$$ H=mc^2\left(1+\frac{p^2}{2m^2c^2}-\frac{p^4}{8m^4c^4}+\cdots\right)=mc^2+\frac{p^2}{2m}-\frac{p^4}{8m^3c^2}+\cdots $$

The first term is the rest energy, the second term is the nonrelativistic kinetic energy, and the third term is the first relativistic correction (it acts to decrease the kinetic energy).

(c) Let $pc=0.10\ \mathrm{MeV}$ and $mc^2=0.511\ \mathrm{MeV}$. The exact kinetic energy is

$$ T=\sqrt{(pc)^2+(mc^2)^2}-mc^2=\sqrt{0.0100+0.2611}\ \mathrm{MeV}-0.511\ \mathrm{MeV}=9.693\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{MeV} $$

The nonrelativistic value is $p^2/(2m)=(pc)^2/(2mc^2)=0.0100/1.022\ \mathrm{MeV}=9.785\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{MeV}$. The difference is $9.785\times10^{-3}-9.693\times10^{-3}=9.2\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{MeV}=92\ \mathrm{eV}$ (about $1\%$ of $T$). The third term in (b) is $-\dfrac{(pc)^4}{8(mc^2)^3}=-\dfrac{1.0\times10^{-4}}{8\times0.1334}\ \mathrm{MeV}=-9.4\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{MeV}=-94\ \mathrm{eV}$, which agrees well with the magnitude of the difference (the remaining few $\mathrm{eV}$ come from still higher-order terms).

∎
